\title{Simple Stochastic Games:\\
A Self-Contained Development of the Value Problem,\\
and the Exact Location of the Remaining Gap}
\author{}
\date{}

\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
We give a self-contained, fully proved development of the theory
surrounding the decision problem \textsc{SSG-Value}: given a simple
stochastic game and a start vertex $v_0$, decide whether
$\val(v_0)\ge\tfrac12$. Everything asserted below is proved from first
principles --- with four imported facts, named where they occur: the
classical determinacy statement of \Cref{thm:determinacy}, and Holt and
Klee's theorem on the orientations that linear programmes induce on
polytopes, used in \Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp}; two textbook facts used in
the algorithms of \Cref{sec:width} and in one descriptive clause of
\Cref{sec:projection}, Legendre's theorem that a close rational
approximant is a convergent (\Cref{lem:round-recover}) and the
Samelson--Thrall--Wesler characterisation of $P$-matrices
(\Cref{prop:lcp}), the latter used nowhere else; and a number of
computations recorded explicitly as measurements --- and every negative
statement is witnessed by an explicit instance verified in exact rational
arithmetic.

\emph{Foundations.} The monotone fixed-point theory of the Shapley
operator, including the identification of $\val$ and of every
$\val_\sigma$ with a \emph{least} fixed point on arbitrary --- not
necessarily stopping --- games (\Cref{thm:lfp-general}), a polynomial
evaluation of $\val_\sigma$ valid without any stopping hypothesis
(\Cref{thm:eval-stopfree}), a denominator bound $2^{a}$ in the number $a$
of average vertices that is attained (\Cref{lem:denominator-sharp}), and
a quantitative stopping transformation with every constant computed
(\Cref{thm:stopping-transform}).

\emph{Polynomial classes.} Arbitrary SSGs in which one colour is absent
from every cycle (\Cref{thm:kacyclic}), a single theorem strictly
stronger than the three separate tractable cases it contains; arbitrary
SSGs whose \emph{escape exponent} is $O(\log N)$ (\Cref{thm:few-escape}),
which improves the average-vertex bound $\mathrm{poly}(N)2^{a}$ of
\Cref{thm:few-avg} by $\Theta(N)$ in the exponent on an explicit family;
games in which no two Max vertices are mutually reachable, and more
generally those whose Max-reachability components are small
(\Cref{thm:bounded-components}); a quantitative class incomparable with
the escape-exponent class and contained in none of the combinatorial
ones, defined by a linear-programming certificate for the rate at
which the two-sided value iteration contracts
(\Cref{thm:escape-class}), with an explicit family inside it and outside
each of the four combinatorial classes and the denominator class
(\Cref{prop:escape-family}; it has modulator number one, so it lies in
the modulator class named below); a class keyed not to
the presentation but to the \emph{answer}, namely the stopping games whose
values share a polynomially bounded denominator $D$
(\Cref{thm:few-denominator}), which rests on the exactness of the
\emph{upward}-rounded Shapley iteration on an arbitrary grid containing
the values (\Cref{thm:alphabet-iteration}) and on the rigidity of the
value alphabet, whose $k$ letters satisfy $D\le2^{k-2}$ sharply
(\Cref{thm:alphabet-rigid,thm:alphabet-denominator}), and which contains a
stopping family with four values, $a=\Theta(N)$ and both players that lies
outside all five of the above (\Cref{prop:fv-family}); a class keyed to a
decomposition, the games with a bounded modulator number, solved by a
fixed-point search over the frozen vertices (\Cref{thm:modulator}) with a
family inside it and outside five of the six classes above
(\Cref{prop:modulator-family}), stopping games of bounded treewidth
being solved in quasipolynomial time (\Cref{thm:qp}); and, in general, a subexponential
Las~Vegas algorithm of expected running time
$e^{2\sqrt n}\,\mathrm{poly}(N)$ (\Cref{thm:subexp}) --- Ludwig's
random-facet bound, reproved here without genericity assumptions --- which
is the strongest general upper bound we obtain.

\emph{Where the difficulty is.} The optimal Max strategies of a stopping
game form a combinatorial subcube (\Cref{thm:opt-subcube}) and every
strategy is joined to it by at most $|\Vmax|$ single strictly improving
switches (\Cref{thm:short-path}), so the whole difficulty lies in
\emph{selecting} switches; and that selection is itself the original
problem, since \textsc{SSG-Value} is equivalent to comparing two vertex
values (\Cref{thm:compare-equivalence}) and a rule resolving one
controlled vertex per round already suffices
(\Cref{thm:decide-one-bit}). The information involved is small --- the
mere order of the average values determines everything
(\Cref{thm:order-determines}, Gimbert and Horn's decoder rediscovered) --- but each bit of it is again an instance
of the problem.

\emph{Mechanisms, and where each stops.} Six sound polynomial-time
comparison mechanisms are developed and measured against one another: an
arithmetic-free simulation preorder (\Cref{sec:simorder}), which beats
every bounded radius and dies on branch compensation, as does its whole
class (\Cref{thm:matching-barrier}); a slack calculus
(\Cref{sec:slack}), which repairs that and inherits a value-iteration
bound on separated parts (\Cref{thm:slack-barrier}); the same calculus
read multiplicatively, the ratio calculus of \Cref{sec:ratio}, whose
average clauses are the harmonic mean and the mediant, which is
incomparable with the additive one pair by pair yet obeys the same
two-sided bound on the same configurations and stalls on the same family
for the same number of rounds
(\Cref{thm:ratio-sandwich,cor:ratio-stall,prop:ratio-incomparable}), so
the barrier is not a fact about additivity --- both bounds holding for the
calculi's own clauses only, since adding transitivity collapses the
witnessing family from $2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds to $4m-3$ for $m\le9$
(\Cref{prop:trans-Hm}), and on stopping games the calculus is in fact
exact in the limit (\Cref{thm:trans-complete}), so what is at stake is a
rate and not an expressive power; the bracket-seeded variant (\Cref{sec:seeded}); and a
linear-programming certificate (\Cref{sec:transport}) that is exact on
successor-closed average parts and therefore settles in one solve both
instances on which the other mechanisms provably fail, while failing in turn,
for a complementary reason, to order the two successors on a seven-vertex game
(\Cref{prop:transport-stalls}) and on the family behind it
(\Cref{thm:transport-barrier}) --- though \Cref{rem:own-successor} shows that
ordering the two successors is a different test from deciding the vertex,
the two readings being incomparable, and that on that game and on the
whole family behind it the certificate decides the vertex at once, while
on the ten-vertex $R$ only the pair reading decides the vertices outside
the free seed, and a seven-vertex game keeps one vertex silent under all
three readings until two others are decided (\Cref{rem:own-stall}). Because those two failures are
complementary, we couple the slack calculus and the transport programme into a feedback loop
(\Cref{sec:hybrid}): the non-convex halves of the controlled rows, which
a linear programme must drop, are exactly what the slack clauses supply,
and the average equalities, which the slack calculus can only reach by
iterating, are what the programme solves at a stroke. On a stopping game
its every closed matrix pins the polytope to the value vector itself
(\Cref{thm:hybrid-complete}), an argument that consults the programme
only at the $4N$ pairs containing a sink (\Cref{cor:hybrid-sink}); it
disposes in one round or two of every stall recorded before it is
introduced, the ten-vertex stall $R$ of \Cref{prop:own-stall} with its
five controlled vertices included --- a fact that means little, since
every other such instance has a single controlled vertex, a class
\Cref{prop:hybrid-onectrl} shows is decided at round two unconditionally
--- while the wedge of \Cref{sec:wedge}, recorded after it, defeats it
for a proved $2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds. Its rate is open. What we can prove about it is
a method for bounding it from below, a shrinking chain of convex
certificates (\Cref{thm:hybrid-convex-barrier}) generalising the box
certificate of \Cref{thm:slack-barrier}, which as
\Cref{rem:hybrid-barrier-lift} shows does not itself lift; and two
applications: a one-player family with two Max vertices wired crosswise on
which one natural family of entries stays uninformative for
$2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds (\Cref{thm:hybrid-lower}) --- though not the
entries a decision rule actually reads
(\Cref{rem:own-successor,rem:hybrid-lower-not-a-refutation}) --- and the
wedge of \Cref{sec:wedge}, on which the entries a decision rule does read
stay uninformative for a proved $2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds
(\Cref{thm:wedge-proved,cor:wedge-count}) --- for the slack, transitive,
transport and hybrid mechanisms, though not for the simulation preorder
or the seeded calculus (\Cref{rem:wedge}). A one-player family on which
the preorder and every propagation mechanism --- slack, closure,
transport, hybrid and ratio, in any combination --- are silent for a
proved $2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds at its one value-distinguishing vertex,
while one policy evaluation decides it, closes the propagation side
(\Cref{sec:bc}); the certificate method now covers the multiplicative
register (\Cref{thm:convex-barrier-both}).

\emph{Barriers.} No potential of polynomial range (\Cref{rem:impedance},
from the classical transfer-impedance identity of \Cref{thm:impedance}); no progress measure of polynomial height
(\Cref{cor:no-height}); no sound rule reading a bounded radius
(\Cref{prop:locality}); every single-switch improving rule capped at $N2^{a}$ switches,
every multi-switch one at $N4^{a}$, and no better bound obtainable from
the value grid
(\Cref{thm:switch-count}); no counter whose bit flips because it is the
only flippable one (\Cref{prop:serialiser}); and no rule whose advantage
comes from resolving the deterministic part of the game, since a
value-preserving and irreducible normalisation deletes exactly that
structure (\Cref{thm:normalform-barrier}) --- nor, more generally, any
rule that solves exactly $O(N^{\alpha})$ average vertices for a fixed
$\alpha<1$, since a longer padding of the same kind blinds every such
rule at once (\Cref{thm:window-barrier}); and no submodular
minimisation argument, in any orientation of either strategy cube
(\Cref{prop:no-submodular}); Condon's damping read as a homotopy in
the survival probability has $2^{\Omega(N)}$ breakpoints, so no
continuation method following its optimal pair is polynomial, and it is
not the bias homotopy (\Cref{prop:discount-fold,cor:two-homotopies}); and
every such bound is a bound on the rounds of a rule, not on information,
since an optimal strategy is found from $|C|+2$ adaptively chosen
evaluations (\Cref{thm:eval-queries}). For the all-switches rule
itself we prove three monotonicity laws
(\Cref{cor:no-return,cor:law-b,cor:law-u}, the first from
\Cref{lem:monotone-law}, the second from \Cref{thm:peak-law}, the third
from the orientation of \Cref{cor:ceiling-general}, with
\Cref{cor:antichain} between them), and an unconditional bound in terms of
the reachability structure of $\Vmax$ (\Cref{thm:component-bound}), and we
locate the exact ceiling the first two laws impose: it is $1,2,4,7,13$
for $m\le5$, while the largest bottom-antipodal height of an acyclic
unique sink orientation --- which, by \Cref{cor:ceiling-general}, every
all-switches run of every stopping game is, degenerate or not --- is
$1,2,4,7,12$ (\Cref{prop:auso-census,prop:hstar-five}) and grows
exponentially: $h^{\ast}(m+2)\ge2h^{\ast}(m)+2$ by a height-doubling
blow-up of the kind Schurr and Szab\'o introduced, proved here from
scratch, its unique-sink, acyclicity and walk clauses machine-checked in
Lean (\Cref{thm:blowup}). So those two
laws are strictly weaker than the axioms they come from; with the third
law added the ceiling drops to $12$ at $m=5$ (reproduced here) and is
reported as $21$ at $m=6$, a measurement (\Cref{rem:flat}). The tallest orientation the
census admits at $m=4$ is realised, by a nondegenerate two-player stopping
game on $97$ vertices (\Cref{prop:auso-seven}), and it needs two players:
nondegenerate one-player improvement orientations are linear-programming
orientations of the occupancy polytope, hence Holt--Klee
(\Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp}), which the height-seven orbit is not
(\Cref{cor:seven-two-player}), and ties do not help at $m=4$
(\Cref{thm:no-seven}); the first blow-up level, and a layer translated by
a single coordinate, are realised by one-player games on $58$ and $100$
vertices, and the second level by a two-player game on $138$ vertices with
one Min vertex (\Cref{rem:blowup-measured,prop:b2-realised}), whose
walk no one-player game has (\Cref{thm:b2-walk}), so the smallest open
instance of the pivot is the third level, of which the outer half is
realised on $194$ vertices while the translated layer remains unrealised
(\Cref{prop:b3-outer}) and out of reach of the pinned shape that realised
the outer half, which provably cannot double (\Cref{cor:pinned-no-doubling});
the height of every blow-up is exactly $2h+2$
(\Cref{prop:blowup-height}). The one-player ceiling is
$1,2,4,6$ for $m\le4$ and at least $10,12,13$ at $m=5,6,7$, the Holt--Klee
ceiling $1,2,4,6,11$ and at least $14,20$ at $m=6,7$, both rows growing by
at least one per dimension
(\Cref{cor:hstar-one,prop:hstar-one-five,prop:hk-records,prop:sink-lift,rem:four-ceilings}),
and the one-player row exceeds the trivial one by a constant factor:
stacking copies of a block behind a freezing bias gives one-player games
with $7k$ Max vertices and runs of length $12k$, a construction whose
run per state never exceeds its best block's
(\Cref{thm:stack,cor:stack-family}); the blow-up
itself doubles while staying Holt--Klee once its outer pair reads a
signal other than the parity, at $m=4$ and $m=5$, and a readout-free
condition stops the iteration on every tall seed examined
(\Cref{prop:blowup-readout,prop:tstar}). At $m=3$ every class is realised,
the sixteen Holt--Klee ones by one-player games and the two others with a
single Min vertex (\Cref{prop:m3-realised,prop:m3-one-min}). The profile cube of every
stopping game is a unique sink orientation, proved here from scratch,
with the Max cube as its sink projection
(\Cref{thm:profile-uso,prop:projection}); it can be cyclic while the
projection cannot, and Holt--Klee constrains the projection not at all
wherever this was checked --- every orientation at $m\le3$, the extremal
orbit at $m=4$, $B^{2}$ at $m=5$ (\Cref{sec:projection}).

We emphasise at the outset what this document does \emph{not} contain: it
does not contain a polynomial-time algorithm for \textsc{SSG-Value}, and
it does not claim one. \Cref{sec:gap} states precisely the missing
statement, and proves that it is equivalent in strength to the target.
\end{abstract}

\tableofcontents

\section{Introduction}

\subsection{The problem}

\begin{definition}[Simple stochastic game]\label{def:ssg}
A \emph{simple stochastic game} (SSG) is a finite directed graph $G$ on a
vertex set
\[
  V \;=\; \Vmax \,\cup\, \Vmin \,\cup\, \Vavg \,\cup\, \{t_0,t_1\},
\]
a disjoint union, in which the two \emph{sinks} $t_0,t_1$ have no outgoing
edges and every other vertex has exactly two outgoing edges (parallel
edges and self-loops are permitted). We write $N := |V|$ and
$n := |\Vmax|$, and we denote the two successors of a non-sink vertex $v$
by $v^{(0)}$ and $v^{(1)}$.

A token is placed on a designated start vertex $v_0$ and moved along
edges. At a vertex of $\Vmax$ the edge is chosen by player Max; at a
vertex of $\Vmin$ by player Min; at a vertex of $\Vavg$ by a fair coin,
independently of everything else. Play stops if a sink is reached. Max
wins if and only if the token reaches $t_1$; every other play, including
every infinite play, is a win for Min.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Strategies and values]\label{def:strategies}
A \emph{positional} (equivalently, pure memoryless) strategy for Max is a
map $\sigma\colon \Vmax\to V$ with $\sigma(v)\in\{v^{(0)},v^{(1)}\}$;
positional strategies $\tau$ for Min are defined analogously on $\Vmin$.
We write $\bar\sigma(v)$ for the successor of $v$ \emph{not} chosen by
$\sigma$, and $\sigma[S]$ for the strategy obtained from $\sigma$ by
flipping the choice at every vertex of $S\subseteq\Vmax$; we abbreviate
$\sigma[\{v\}]$ to $\sigma[v]$.

A pair $(\sigma,\tau)$ of positional strategies turns $G$ into a finite
Markov chain $M_{\sigma,\tau}$: from $v\in\Vmax$ the token moves to
$\sigma(v)$ with probability $1$, from $v\in\Vmin$ to $\tau(v)$ with
probability $1$, and from $v\in\Vavg$ to each of $v^{(0)},v^{(1)}$ with
probability $\tfrac12$ each. Let
\[
  v_{\sigma,\tau}(u)\;:=\;\Pr\nolimits^{M_{\sigma,\tau}}_{u}\bigl[\text{the token reaches } t_1\bigr].
\]
The \emph{value} of $G$ at $u$ is
$\val(u):=\max_\sigma\min_\tau v_{\sigma,\tau}(u)$, the maximum and
minimum ranging over positional strategies. For a fixed Max strategy
$\sigma$ we write
\[
  \val_\sigma\;:=\;\min_\tau v_{\sigma,\tau}
\]
for the pointwise minimum over positional Min strategies --- the value
Max can guarantee by committing to $\sigma$. By \Cref{thm:determinacy}
applied to the one-player game obtained by freezing $\sigma$, the minimum
is attained simultaneously at all vertices by a single positional $\tau$,
so $\val_\sigma$ is itself of the form $v_{\sigma,\tau}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{problem}[\textsc{SSG-Value}]\label{prob:main}
Given an SSG $G$ and a start vertex $v_0$, decide whether
$\val(v_0)\ge\tfrac12$.
\end{problem}

The first of the four external facts we import is the following
classical determinacy statement; the other three are Holt and Klee's
theorem (\Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp}), Legendre's theorem on convergents
(\Cref{lem:round-recover}) and the Samelson--Thrall--Wesler
characterisation of $P$-matrices (\Cref{prop:lcp}), and everything else
in this document is proved from scratch.

\begin{theorem}[Positional determinacy; Shapley, Condon]\label{thm:determinacy}
For every SSG and every vertex $u$,
\[
  \max_\sigma\min_\tau v_{\sigma,\tau}(u)
  \;=\;\min_\tau\max_\sigma v_{\sigma,\tau}(u),
\]
the extrema ranging over positional strategies, and this common value
equals the value of the game when both players are additionally allowed
arbitrary history-dependent randomised strategies. Moreover the
extrema are attained simultaneously at all vertices by a single pair
$(\sigma^\ast,\tau^\ast)$ of positional strategies.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{What is proved here, and what is not}

\Cref{prob:main} is not resolved below. What is established is a complete
and self-contained chain of results that isolates the obstruction
exactly. The logical skeleton is:

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item \Cref{sec:operators,sec:stopping}: the Shapley operator, its
  monotonicity, and --- for \emph{stopping} games --- a proof that its
  $N$-th iterate, and that of each one-player restriction $T_\sigma$, is a
  strict contraction, so that each has a unique fixed point
  (\Cref{thm:contraction}); determinacy and the identification of those
  fixed points with the values are then proved rather than imported
  (\Cref{thm:stopping-determinacy}). The operator $T$ itself is in general
  not a contraction.
\item \Cref{sec:transform}: Condon's stopping transformation made fully
  quantitative --- \Cref{prob:main} on an arbitrary SSG reduces to a
  shifted threshold question on a stopping SSG of size
  $O(N(|\Vavg|+\log N))$ (\Cref{thm:stopping-transform}) and, by the
  threshold gadget of \Cref{cor:stopping-reduction}, to \Cref{prob:main}
  itself on a stopping SSG. All constants are computed.
\item \Cref{sec:special}: polynomial special cases --- arbitrary SSGs
  with $O(\log N)$ average vertices (\Cref{thm:few-avg}), obtained from a
  positional escape estimate that needs no stopping hypothesis and whose
  rate is attained (\Cref{rem:few-avg-tight}), and then the sharper
  exponent given by counting only the average vertices at which a single
  forced descent branches (\Cref{thm:few-escape}, separated from the
  first by $\Theta(N)$ in \Cref{prop:fk-family}); games no cycle of which
  meets $\Vavg$ (\Cref{thm:avg-acyclic}); games no cycle of which meets
  $\Vmax\cup\Vmin$ (\Cref{thm:player-free}); one-player games
  (\Cref{thm:one-player}); and the single theorem containing all three of
  those and strictly stronger than them together, that it suffices for
  any \emph{one} of the three colours to be absent from every cycle
  (\Cref{thm:kacyclic,prop:kacyclic-strict}); the escape class, defined by
  a linear-programming certificate for the contraction rate
  (\Cref{thm:escape-class}); the value alphabet, with the class of
  stopping games whose values share a small denominator
  (\Cref{sec:alphabet}, \Cref{thm:few-denominator}); and the width
  classes, the modulator class in polynomial time and bounded treewidth
  in quasipolynomial time (\Cref{sec:width}, \Cref{thm:modulator,thm:qp}).
\item \Cref{sec:structure}: the structure of the optimal set.
  \Cref{thm:opt-subcube} identifies $\Opt$ with a subcube;
  \Cref{thm:short-path} shows every strategy is joined to $\Opt$ by an
  improving path of length at most $n$. Explicit instances
  (\Cref{ex:nonstopping-nonsubcube,ex:pi-fails}) show that the stopping
  hypothesis is not removable.
\item \Cref{sec:refutations}: refutations and barriers, each with an
  explicit instance verified in exact arithmetic, that eliminate specific
  strategies of proof --- among them an acyclic one-player ladder on
  which the least-index and smallest-gap rules take $2^{(N-2)/2}-1$
  improving switches (\Cref{thm:ladder}), although the game is solved by
  back-substitution in linear time and \Cref{thm:short-path} provides a
  route of length $n$.
\item \Cref{sec:selection}: a proof that the problem is polynomial-time
  equivalent to comparing two vertex values, and that $n$ non-adaptive
  comparisons determine an optimal strategy
  (\Cref{thm:compare-equivalence}).
\item \Cref{sec:randomfacet}: the strongest positive algorithmic result
  proved here --- a Las~Vegas algorithm of expected running time
  $e^{2\sqrt n}\,\mathrm{poly}(N)$ (\Cref{thm:subexp}), subexponential but
  not polynomial.
\item \Cref{sec:allsw-laws}: three monotonicity laws obeyed by every
  all-switches run (\Cref{cor:no-return,cor:law-b,cor:law-u}, from
  \Cref{lem:monotone-law}, \Cref{thm:peak-law} and
  \Cref{cor:ceiling-general}), an unconditional bound on its length in terms of
  the reachability structure of $\Vmax$ (\Cref{thm:component-bound}), and
  the polynomial class that bound defines (\Cref{thm:bounded-components}),
  which \Cref{prop:k1-family} shows meets none of the four combinatorial
  classes of \Cref{sec:special}; then the identification of every
  all-switches run with a bottom-antipodal walk on an acyclic unique sink
  orientation (\Cref{prop:allsw-auso,thm:flat-resolution,cor:ceiling-general}),
  the census and the height-doubling blow-up
  (\Cref{prop:auso-census,prop:hstar-five,thm:blowup}), the realised
  orientations (\Cref{prop:auso-seven}, \Cref{rem:blowup-measured}), the
  one-player results (\Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp,cor:seven-two-player,thm:no-seven})
  and the two-player cube (\Cref{prop:gsharp-bigcube}).
\item \Cref{sec:gap}: the exact statement of what is missing, together
  with a proof that supplying it is equivalent to resolving
  \Cref{prob:main}. The same holds for a formally weaker demand ---
  resolving one vertex per round, by any mechanism, on a game one may
  simplify in between (\Cref{thm:decide-one-bit}) --- while
  \Cref{prop:locality} shows the single bit so demanded cannot be read
  off any bounded-radius neighbourhood, and \Cref{prop:pdc-separation}
  separates that demand from the switching-rule one on an explicit
  game.
\end{enumerate}

\section{The Shapley operator}\label{sec:operators}

Throughout, vectors are indexed by $V$ and compared pointwise: $x\le y$
means $x(u)\le y(u)$ for all $u$. Let
\[
  \mathcal{X}\;:=\;\bigl\{x\in[0,1]^V \;:\; x(t_1)=1,\; x(t_0)=0\bigr\}.
\]

\begin{definition}\label{def:shapley}
The \emph{Shapley operator} $T\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}$ is defined
by $(Tx)(t_1)=1$, $(Tx)(t_0)=0$ and, for a non-sink $v$,
\[
  (Tx)(v)\;=\;
  \begin{cases}
    \max\bigl(x(v^{(0)}),\,x(v^{(1)})\bigr), & v\in\Vmax,\\[2pt]
    \min\bigl(x(v^{(0)}),\,x(v^{(1)})\bigr), & v\in\Vmin,\\[2pt]
    \tfrac12\bigl(x(v^{(0)})+x(v^{(1)})\bigr), & v\in\Vavg.
  \end{cases}
\]
For a Max strategy $\sigma$, the operator $T_\sigma$ is defined by the
same formulas except that the $\Vmax$ line is replaced by
$(T_\sigma x)(v)=x(\sigma(v))$. For a pair $(\sigma,\tau)$, the operator
$T_{\sigma,\tau}$ additionally replaces the $\Vmin$ line by
$(T_{\sigma,\tau}x)(v)=x(\tau(v))$; it is affine.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Monotone, nonexpansive]\label{lem:monotone}
$T$, every $T_\sigma$ and every $T_{\sigma,\tau}$ map $\mathcal{X}$ into
$\mathcal{X}$, are monotone ($x\le y\Rightarrow Tx\le Ty$), and are
nonexpansive in the supremum norm:
$\lVert Tx-Ty\rVert_\infty\le\lVert x-y\rVert_\infty$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Each coordinate of $Tx$ is a maximum, a minimum, or a convex combination
of coordinates of $x$, and each of those three operations preserves
$[0,1]$, is monotone, and is nonexpansive: for a maximum,
$|\max(a_1,a_2)-\max(b_1,b_2)|\le\max_i|a_i-b_i|$, and symmetrically for
a minimum; for a convex combination,
$\bigl|\tfrac12(a_1+a_2)-\tfrac12(b_1+b_2)\bigr|\le\max_i|a_i-b_i|$. The
sink coordinates are pinned and contribute $0$ to the difference.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Profile values are least fixed points]\label{lem:lfp}
For every positional pair $(\sigma,\tau)$, the vector $v_{\sigma,\tau}$
is the least fixed point of $T_{\sigma,\tau}$ in $\mathcal{X}$;
equivalently, $v_{\sigma,\tau}=\min\{x\in\mathcal{X}:T_{\sigma,\tau}x\le x\}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Reachability probabilities in a finite Markov chain satisfy the
corresponding linear system, so $v_{\sigma,\tau}$ is a fixed point of
$T_{\sigma,\tau}$. Let $x\in\mathcal{X}$ satisfy
$T_{\sigma,\tau}x\le x$. Write $p_k(u)$ for the probability of reaching
$t_1$ from $u$ within $k$ steps; then $p_0=\bbone_{\{t_1\}}\le x$, and by
induction using monotonicity
$p_{k+1}=T_{\sigma,\tau}\,p_k\le T_{\sigma,\tau}x\le x$. Since
$p_k\uparrow v_{\sigma,\tau}$ we get $v_{\sigma,\tau}\le x$. As
$v_{\sigma,\tau}$ is itself a fixed point, it is the least one and also
the least prefixed point; this is the Knaster--Tarski characterisation
made explicit.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:no-postfixed}
The dual statement fails: $x\le T_{\sigma,\tau}x$ does \emph{not} imply
$x\le v_{\sigma,\tau}$. Taking a single average vertex whose two edges
both return to itself, and $x$ equal to $1$ at that vertex (and pinned at
the sinks), gives $T_{\sigma,\tau}x=x$ while
$v_{\sigma,\tau}=0$. This is exactly the phenomenon that the stopping
hypothesis of the next section removes, and it is why several arguments
below are stated for stopping games. \Cref{ex:pi-fails} shows the same
failure doing real damage to an algorithm.
\end{remark}
\begin{lemma}[First-passage decomposition]\label{lem:excursion}
Let $(\sigma,\tau)$ be a positional pair of an arbitrary SSG, let
$M_{\sigma,\tau}$ be the induced Markov chain, and fix a non-sink vertex
$v$. For $u\in V$ put
\[
  q(u):=\Pr_u[\,t_1\text{ is reached strictly before }v\,],\qquad
  r(u):=\Pr_u[\,v\text{ is reached strictly before }t_1\,].
\]
Reaching includes time $0$: a walk started at a vertex has reached it, so
$q(v)=0$ and $r(v)=1$.
Then \emph{(a)} $q$ and $r$ are unchanged if the out-edges of $v$ are
altered --- in particular they are the same for $(\sigma,\tau)$ and for
any pair differing from it only at $v$; \emph{(b)}
$v_{\sigma,\tau}(u)=q(u)+r(u)\,v_{\sigma,\tau}(v)$ for every $u$; and
\emph{(c)} $q(u)+r(u)\le1$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(c) The two events are disjoint. (b) Since $t_1$ is absorbing,
$\{\text{reach }t_1\}$ is the disjoint union of
$\{t_1\text{ before }v\}$ and
$\{v\text{ before }t_1\}\cap\theta_{H_v}^{-1}\{\text{reach }t_1\}$, where
$H_v$ is the hitting time of $v$ and $\theta$ the shift; the strong
Markov property at $H_v$ gives the second term probability
$r(u)v_{\sigma,\tau}(v)$. (a) Both events are determined by the
trajectory up to and including its first visit to $v$, i.e.\ by the law
of the stopped process $(X_{k\wedge H_v})_{k\ge0}$, and that law is
determined by the transition rows at vertices other than $v$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Values are least fixed points, with no stopping hypothesis]\label{thm:lfp-general}
Let $G$ be an arbitrary SSG. Then
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item for every positional Max strategy $\sigma$,
  $T_\sigma\val_\sigma=\val_\sigma$ and
  $\val_\sigma=\min\{x\in\mathcal{X}:T_\sigma x\le x\}$;
\item $T\val=\val$ and $\val=\min\{x\in\mathcal{X}:Tx\le x\}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(a) Let $G_\sigma$ be $G$ with every $v\in\Vmax$ retyped as an average
vertex both of whose out-edges lead to $\sigma(v)$; the move out of $v$
is forced, so $G_\sigma$ and $G$ induce the same Markov chain for every
$\tau$, whence $v^{G_\sigma}_\tau=v^{G}_{\sigma,\tau}$. As $G_\sigma$ has
no Max vertices, \Cref{thm:determinacy} applied to it yields a positional
$\tau^\ast$ with $F:=\val_\sigma=v_{\sigma,\tau^\ast}$, the minimum being
attained at every vertex simultaneously.

By \Cref{lem:lfp}, $v_{\sigma,\tau^\ast}$ is a fixed point of
$T_{\sigma,\tau^\ast}$. At average vertices, at sinks and at Max vertices
--- where $T_\sigma$ reads $\sigma(v)$, exactly as $T_{\sigma,\tau^\ast}$
does --- this already gives $(T_\sigma F)(v)=F(v)$. Let $v\in\Vmin$ have
successors $w_0,w_1$. Then
$F(v)=F(\tau^\ast(v))\ge\min\{F(w_0),F(w_1)\}=(T_\sigma F)(v)$, so only
$F(v)\le F(w)$ for both successors $w$ remains.

Fix such a $w$ and put $\tau':=\tau^\ast[v\mapsto w]$. Let $q,r$ be as in
\Cref{lem:excursion} for $(\sigma,\tau^\ast)$ and the vertex $v$; by
part~(a) of that lemma they are also the $q,r$ of $(\sigma,\tau')$. Since
$\tau'(v)=w$,
\[
  v_{\sigma,\tau'}(v)=v_{\sigma,\tau'}(w)=q(w)+r(w)\,v_{\sigma,\tau'}(v).
\]
If $r(w)=1$ then $q(w)=0$ by \Cref{lem:excursion}(c), and
\Cref{lem:excursion}(b) for $(\sigma,\tau^\ast)$ gives
$F(w)=q(w)+r(w)F(v)=F(v)$. If $r(w)<1$ then the display gives
$v_{\sigma,\tau'}(v)=q(w)/(1-r(w))$; as $F$ is the pointwise minimum over
positional Min strategies, $F(v)\le v_{\sigma,\tau'}(v)$, i.e.\
$F(v)(1-r(w))\le q(w)$, and \Cref{lem:excursion}(b) for
$(\sigma,\tau^\ast)$ gives
\[
  F(w)=q(w)+r(w)F(v)\;\ge\;F(v)(1-r(w))+r(w)F(v)=F(v).
\]
Either way $F(v)\le F(w)$, so $T_\sigma F=F$.

For minimality, let $T_\sigma x\le x$ and let $\tau_x$ choose at each Min
vertex a successor minimising $x$. Then $T_{\sigma,\tau_x}x\le T_\sigma
x\le x$, since the two operators agree except at Min vertices, where
$(T_{\sigma,\tau_x}x)(v)=x(\tau_x(v))=\min\{x(w_0),x(w_1)\}=(T_\sigma
x)(v)$. By \Cref{lem:lfp}, $v_{\sigma,\tau_x}\le x$, hence
$\val_\sigma\le v_{\sigma,\tau_x}\le x$.

(b) By \Cref{thm:determinacy} choose $(\sigma^\ast,\tau^\ast)$ attaining
both extrema, so that $\val=v_{\sigma^\ast,\tau^\ast}=\val_{\sigma^\ast}$
and also $\val=\max_\sigma v_{\sigma,\tau^\ast}$. At average vertices and
sinks $T\val=\val$ is immediate from (a). At $v\in\Vmin$, (a) applied to
$\sigma^\ast$ gives $\val(v)=(T_{\sigma^\ast}\val)(v)=(T\val)(v)$. At
$v\in\Vmax$, (a) gives $\val(v)=\val(\sigma^\ast(v))$, whence
$\val(v)\le\max\{\val(v^{(0)}),\val(v^{(1)})\}$; and the reverse
inequality $\val(v)\ge\val(w)$ for both successors $w$ is the argument of
part (a) in the game $G^{\tau^\ast}$ with Min frozen, where
$F':=\max_\sigma v_{\sigma,\tau^\ast}=\val$ is attained simultaneously by
$\sigma^\ast$; written out: for $v\in\Vmax$ and a successor $w$ put
$\sigma':=\sigma^\ast[v\mapsto w]$ and let $q,r$ be those of
\Cref{lem:excursion} for $(\sigma^\ast,\tau^\ast)$ and $v$, which by
part~(a) of that lemma are also those of $(\sigma',\tau^\ast)$. If
$r(w)=1$ then $q(w)=0$ and \Cref{lem:excursion}(b) gives
$F'(w)=q(w)+r(w)F'(v)=F'(v)$; if $r(w)<1$ then
$F'(v)\ge v_{\sigma',\tau^\ast}(v)=q(w)/(1-r(w))$, so
$F'(w)=q(w)+r(w)F'(v)\le F'(v)(1-r(w))+r(w)F'(v)=F'(v)$. No duality
between the players is used. Minimality is as in (a): given
$Tx\le x$, the same $\tau_x$ satisfies $T_{\sigma,\tau_x}x\le Tx\le x$
for \emph{every} $\sigma$, so $v_{\sigma,\tau_x}\le x$ for every $\sigma$
and therefore
$\val=\max_\sigma\min_\tau v_{\sigma,\tau}\le\max_\sigma
v_{\sigma,\tau_x}\le x$.
\end{proof}

The least-fixed-point description has an algorithmic consequence that the
stopping hypothesis is usually invoked for: a Max strategy can be
\emph{evaluated} exactly, in polynomial time, on an arbitrary SSG. The
linear program that does this is not the obvious one, and the obvious one
is unsound. It is, however, the textbook programme for minimal reachability
probabilities in a Markov decision process, whose zero set must be computed
by a graph algorithm before the programme is solved, as in de Alfaro's
thesis and in Baier and Katoen's treatment; it is rederived here because
the least-fixed-point identity it rests on has just been proved for
arbitrary games.

\begin{definition}[Trapping set]\label{def:trap}
Let $\sigma$ be a positional Max strategy. Let $Z_\sigma$ be the largest
set $U\subseteq V\setminus\{t_1\}$ such that every $v\in U\cap\Vmin$ has
\emph{some} successor in $U$, every $v\in U\cap\Vavg$ has \emph{both}
successors in $U$, and every $v\in U\cap\Vmax$ has $\sigma(v)\in U$. Note
$t_0$ is permitted in $U$; only $t_1$ is excluded. The union of two such
sets is again one, so $Z_\sigma$ exists, and iterated deletion computes it
in $O(N^{2})$ steps.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[The trapping set is the zero set]\label{lem:trap}
For every SSG and every positional $\sigma$,
$Z_\sigma=\{v:\val_\sigma(v)=0\}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
($\subseteq$) Let Min play, at each Min vertex of $Z_\sigma$, a successor
inside $Z_\sigma$. By the closure conditions the token started in
$Z_\sigma$ never leaves it, whatever Max's frozen choices and the coins
do; since $t_1\notin Z_\sigma$ it is never reached, so
$\val_\sigma(v)=0$.
($\supseteq$) If $\val_\sigma(v)=0$, choose a positional $\tau$ with
$v_{\sigma,\tau}(v)=0$ and let $R$ be the set of vertices reachable from
$v$ in the chain $M_{\sigma,\tau}$. Then $t_1\notin R$: a finite path to
$t_1$ would have positive probability. And $R$ satisfies the three
closure conditions, since it is closed under the chain's transitions.
Hence $R\subseteq Z_\sigma$ and in particular $v\in Z_\sigma$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Trapped-class comparison]\label{lem:trapped-comparison}
Let $(\sigma,\tau)$ be a positional pair of an arbitrary SSG and let
$x\in\mathcal{X}$ satisfy $x\le T_{\sigma,\tau}x$. If $x$ vanishes on
every sink-free bottom class of the chain $M_{\sigma,\tau}$ --- every
bottom strongly connected component of the chain digraph containing no
sink --- then $x\le v_{\sigma,\tau}$ pointwise.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
On $\mathcal{X}$ the operator $T_{\sigma,\tau}$ acts as the stochastic
matrix $P$ of the chain with the sinks made absorbing, so $x\le Px$ and, $P$
being nonnegative, $x\le P^{k}x$ for every $k$. Split
$(P^{k}x)(u)=\sum_w P^{k}(u,w)x(w)$ by destination. The term $w=t_1$
contributes $\Pr_u[X_k=t_1]$, which increases to $v_{\sigma,\tau}(u)$
because $t_1$ is absorbing; the terms $w=t_0$ and $w$ in a sink-free
bottom class contribute $0$, since $x$ vanishes there; and the remaining
mass is $\Pr_u[X_k\notin\text{any bottom class}]$, which tends to $0$,
because from every vertex some path of length at most $N$ reaches a
bottom class in the condensation and every edge of the chain has
probability at least $\tfrac12$, so that mass is at most
$(1-2^{-N})^{\lfloor k/N\rfloor}$. Letting $k\to\infty$ gives
$x(u)\le v_{\sigma,\tau}(u)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Stopping-free evaluation]\label{thm:eval-stopfree}
Let $G$ be an arbitrary SSG and $\sigma$ a positional Max strategy. Then
$\val_\sigma$ is the unique optimum of
\[
\begin{aligned}
  \text{maximise } &\textstyle\sum_{u\in V} x(u)\quad\text{subject to }
     x\in[0,1]^V,\ x(t_1)=1,\ x(t_0)=0,\\
  &x(v)=x(\sigma(v)) && (v\in\Vmax),\\
  &x(v)=\tfrac12\bigl(x(v^{(0)})+x(v^{(1)})\bigr) && (v\in\Vavg),\\
  &x(v)\le x(v^{(0)}),\quad x(v)\le x(v^{(1)}) && (v\in\Vmin),\\
  &x(v)=0 && (v\in Z_\sigma),
\end{aligned}
\]
and is therefore computable exactly in polynomial time, as a rational
vector with common denominator at most $2^{a}$. Dropping the last line
recovers the program \eqref{eq:lp} used later for stopping games.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Feasibility: $\val_\sigma$ is a fixed point of $T_\sigma$
(\Cref{thm:lfp-general}(a)), which gives the equalities at $\Vmax$ and
$\Vavg$ and $x(v)=\min\le x(v^{(i)})$ at $\Vmin$; it vanishes on
$Z_\sigma$ by \Cref{lem:trap}. Domination: let $x$ be feasible and let
$\tau$ be any positional Min strategy. The $\Vmin$ inequalities give
$x\le T_{\sigma,\tau}x$. Every sink-free bottom class $B$ of
$M_{\sigma,\tau}$ is closed under the chain and avoids $t_1$, so it
satisfies the closure conditions of \Cref{def:trap} and $B\subseteq
Z_\sigma$; hence $x$ vanishes on $B$, and \Cref{lem:trapped-comparison}
gives $x\le v_{\sigma,\tau}$. As $\tau$ was arbitrary,
$x\le\min_\tau v_{\sigma,\tau}=\val_\sigma$. So $\val_\sigma$ is feasible
and dominates every feasible point, hence is the unique maximiser of the
sum. The denominator bound is \Cref{lem:denominator-sharp}, since
$\val_\sigma=v_{\sigma,\tau}$ for some positional $\tau$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:lp-needs-zeros}
The added constraints are not a technicality. Without them the program is
\emph{unsound} on non-stopping games: for the one-vertex game
$m\to\{m,t_1\}$ with $m\in\Vmin$ one has $\val_\sigma(m)=0$, since Min
loops for ever, while $x(m)=1$ satisfies both inequalities
$x(m)\le x(m)$ and $x(m)\le x(t_1)$ and is returned by the
maximum-sum program. This is exactly why the program without the zero rows is
invoked in this document only for stopping games (\eqref{eq:lp} below),
where \Cref{cor:comparison} supplies the domination directly, so the zero
rows are redundant there ($Z_\sigma$ itself is never empty --- it contains
$t_0$ --- and on a stopping game it may contain non-sinks of value $0$). The
asymmetry is real: on the Min-frozen side, deleting the zero set does
\emph{not} restore uniqueness --- for $u\in\Vmax$ with $u\to\{u,c\}$ and
$c\to\{t_0,t_1\}$ every value in $[\tfrac12,1]$ at $u$ is a fixed point of
$T^\tau$ and the zero set is $\{t_0\}$ --- and the correct instrument there
is the
\emph{minimum}-sum program of \Cref{thm:one-player}.
\end{remark}


\begin{remark}\label{rem:lfp-scope}
\Cref{thm:lfp-general} is proved by a first-passage decomposition rather
than by exchanging a limit with an infimum, which is what makes it
available without a stopping hypothesis; the excursion identity of
\Cref{lem:excursion} is exactly what replaces that exchange. Only the
\emph{prefixed} half survives in general: $Tx\le x$ implies $\val\le x$,
whereas $x\le Tx$ does \emph{not} imply $x\le\val$ without stopping, as
the one-vertex game $c\to\{c,c\}$ shows --- there $T$ is the identity, so
$x\equiv1$ satisfies $x\le Tx$ while $\val=0$. \Cref{cor:comparison}
therefore keeps its stopping hypothesis on one side, and the certificates
of \Cref{sec:selection} are unaffected.
\end{remark}

\section{Stopping games}\label{sec:stopping}

\begin{definition}\label{def:stopping}
An SSG $G$ is \emph{stopping} if for every positional pair
$(\sigma,\tau)$ and every vertex $u$, the chain $M_{\sigma,\tau}$ reaches
$\{t_0,t_1\}$ from $u$ with probability $1$.
\end{definition}

The absorption estimate below must hold not merely for positional pairs
but for arbitrary, possibly history-dependent and randomised, play: it is
applied inside a finite-horizon game whose optimal strategies are
time-dependent. We therefore prove it by an attractor argument, which is
insensitive to how the players choose.

\begin{definition}[Sink attractor]\label{def:attractor}
Put $A_0:=\{t_0,t_1\}$ and, for $j\ge0$,
\[
  A_{j+1}\;:=\;A_j\;\cup\;\bigl\{v\in\Vavg:\{v^{(0)},v^{(1)}\}\cap A_j\ne\emptyset\bigr\}
  \;\cup\;\bigl\{v\in\Vmax\cup\Vmin:\{v^{(0)},v^{(1)}\}\subseteq A_j\bigr\}.
\]
The sets $A_j$ increase, so they stabilise at $A_\infty:=A_{N}$. For
$u\in A_\infty$ write $\ell(u):=\min\{j:u\in A_j\}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform absorption]\label{lem:absorption}
Write $a:=|\Vavg|$ and let $G$ be stopping. Then $A_\infty=V$, and for
every vertex $u$ and \emph{every} pair of strategies --- positional or
history-dependent, deterministic or randomised --- the probability of
reaching a sink from $u$ within $N$ steps is at least $2^{-a}$.
Consequently the absorption time $T_{\mathrm{abs}}$ satisfies
$\Pr_u[T_{\mathrm{abs}}>jN]\le(1-2^{-a})^j$ and
$\mathbb{E}_u[T_{\mathrm{abs}}]\le N2^{a}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\emph{$A_\infty=V$.} Suppose not, and set $S:=V\setminus A_\infty$, which
is nonempty and contains no sink. If $v\in S\cap\Vavg$ had a successor in
$A_\infty=A_N$ then $v$ would lie in $A_{N+1}=A_\infty$; so both
successors of such a $v$ lie in $S$. If $v\in S\cap(\Vmax\cup\Vmin)$ had
both successors in $A_\infty$ then again $v\in A_\infty$; so at least one
successor of such a $v$ lies in $S$. Choose a positional pair that at
every controlled vertex of $S$ selects a successor inside $S$ (and
arbitrarily elsewhere). Under that pair the token started in $S$ never
leaves $S$, hence never reaches a sink --- contradicting the assumption
that $G$ is stopping.

\emph{The estimate.} For a vertex $u$ let
$A(u):=\bigl|\{w\in\Vavg:\ell(w)\le\ell(u)\}\bigr|\le a$. We show by
induction on $\ell(u)$ that from $u$, under every strategy pair, a sink is
reached within $\ell(u)\le N$ steps with probability at least
$2^{-A(u)}$.

For $\ell(u)=0$ the vertex is already a sink. Let $\ell(u)=j+1$. If
$u\in\Vmax\cup\Vmin$ then \emph{both} successors lie in $A_j$, so whatever
the player does --- at any time, after any history, with any
randomisation --- the token moves in one step to some $w$ with
$\ell(w)\le j$; by induction it then reaches a sink within $j$ further
steps with probability at least $2^{-A(w)}\ge 2^{-A(u)}$, the last
inequality because $\ell(w)\le\ell(u)$ gives $A(w)\le A(u)$. If
$u\in\Vavg$ then at least one successor $w$ has $\ell(w)\le j$ and is
taken with probability $\tfrac12$, so the probability is at least
$\tfrac12\cdot2^{-A(w)}$; here $u$ itself is an average vertex with
$\ell(u)>\ell(w)$, so $u$ is counted in $A(u)$ but not in $A(w)$, giving
$A(u)\ge A(w)+1$ and hence $\tfrac12\cdot2^{-A(w)}\ge2^{-A(u)}$.

Since $A(u)\le a$ for every $u$, the probability of reaching a sink within
$\ell(u)\le N$ steps is at least $2^{-a}$, uniformly over all strategies.

Applying this to consecutive blocks of $N$ steps and using the tower
property gives $\Pr_u[T_{\mathrm{abs}}>jN]\le(1-2^{-a})^j$, whence
$\mathbb{E}[T_{\mathrm{abs}}]\le N\sum_{j\ge0}(1-2^{-a})^j=N2^{a}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Two features of this bound are used later. First, it is
\emph{strategy-independent}, which is what allows it to be invoked inside
the finite-horizon game of \Cref{thm:contraction}, where optimal play is
time-dependent. Second, the exponent is $a=|\Vavg|$ rather than $N$: only
average vertices branch, and a level-decreasing descent meets each at
most once. The second point is what makes the constants in
\Cref{thm:stopping-transform,thm:few-avg} depend on the number of average
vertices rather than on the size of the game.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Contraction and uniqueness]\label{thm:contraction}
Let $G$ be stopping and $a=|\Vavg|$. Then for all $x,y\in\mathcal{X}$,
\[
  \lVert T^{N}x-T^{N}y\rVert_\infty\;\le\;\bigl(1-2^{-a}\bigr)\,\lVert x-y\rVert_\infty ,
\]
and the same holds for $T_\sigma$ and for $T_{\sigma,\tau}$. Consequently
each of these operators has a \emph{unique} fixed point in
$\mathcal{X}$, and every orbit converges to it. For $T_{\sigma,\tau}$
that fixed point is $v_{\sigma,\tau}$; the identification of the fixed
points of $T$ and of $T_\sigma$ with the game values is
\Cref{thm:stopping-determinacy} below, which uses only the uniqueness
established here.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Unfolding the definition $N$ times, backward induction shows that
$(T^{N}x)(u)$ is the value of the $N$-stage game started at $u$ in which
a play absorbed at $t_1$ (resp.\ $t_0$) is paid $1$ (resp.\ $0$) and a
play not absorbed within $N$ steps is paid $x(\cdot)$ at its current
vertex. In that finite-horizon game both players may use time-dependent
strategies, and backward induction produces optimal ones. Both facts are
finite inductions and use no determinacy theorem: with $V_0:=x$ on the
non-sinks and the sinks paid $0$ and $1$, let $V_{k+1}(u)$ be the maximum,
minimum or mean of $V_k$ over the successors of $u$ according to the kind
of $u$; a player who at each stage moves to a successor extremising the
$V$ of the remaining horizon guarantees $V_N(u)$ against every
history-dependent randomised strategy of the opponent, so the $N$-stage
game has the value $V_N=T^{N}x$. Allowing time-dependent strategies is
essential, since the value over \emph{positional} strategies alone can be
strictly smaller: on the stopping game with $v_0,v_1\in\Vmax$,
$v_2\in\Vavg$, $v_3\in\Vmin$, $v_0\to(v_1,v_2)$, $v_1\to(v_2,v_3)$,
$v_2\to(t_0,v_1)$, $v_3\to(v_2,t_0)$ and $x=(\tfrac12,1,\tfrac14,\tfrac12)$
on the non-sinks, $(T^{6}x)(v_2)=\tfrac1{16}$, while over positional pairs
$\max_\sigma\min_\tau$ of the six-stage payoff at $v_2$ is $\tfrac1{32}$,
in exact arithmetic.

Let $\Sigma_N$ denote the set of all (history-dependent, randomised)
strategy pairs in the $N$-stage game, and for $\pi\in\Sigma_N$ write
$K_\pi(u,\cdot)$ for the law of the position after $N$ steps. The
$x$-payoff and the $y$-payoff of a fixed $\pi$ differ by
\[
  \Bigl|\sum_{w\ \mathrm{non\text{-}sink}} K_\pi(u,w)\bigl(x(w)-y(w)\bigr)\Bigr|
  \;\le\;\Pr\nolimits_{\pi}\bigl[T_{\mathrm{abs}}>N\bigr]\cdot\lVert x-y\rVert_\infty
  \;\le\;\bigl(1-2^{-a}\bigr)\lVert x-y\rVert_\infty ,
\]
where the last inequality is \Cref{lem:absorption}, which holds uniformly
over $\pi\in\Sigma_N$ precisely because its proof is
strategy-independent. Two zero-sum games on the same strategy sets whose
payoff functions differ pointwise by at most $\varepsilon$ have values
differing by at most $\varepsilon$; applying this with
$\varepsilon=(1-2^{-a})\lVert x-y\rVert_\infty$ gives the displayed
estimate. The arguments for $T_\sigma$ and $T_{\sigma,\tau}$ are
identical with one or both players' choices frozen.

A map whose $N$-th iterate is a strict contraction on the complete metric
space $\mathcal{X}$ has a unique fixed point, to which all orbits
converge. For $T_{\sigma,\tau}$ that fixed point is $v_{\sigma,\tau}$ by
\Cref{lem:lfp}. The identification of the fixed points of $T_\sigma$ and
$T$ with $\val_\sigma$ and $\val$ is \Cref{thm:stopping-determinacy}
below, which is proved from the uniqueness just established and does not
presuppose it.
\end{proof}

For stopping games we do not need to import \Cref{thm:determinacy}: both
determinacy and the identification of the fixed points with the values
follow from \Cref{thm:contraction} by a verification argument.

\begin{theorem}[Determinacy for stopping games]\label{thm:stopping-determinacy}
Let $G$ be stopping, let $w^\ast$ be the unique fixed point of $T$ and,
for a Max strategy $\sigma$, let $y_\sigma$ be the unique fixed point of
$T_\sigma$ (both exist by \Cref{thm:contraction}). Let $\sigma^\ast$
choose at each $v\in\Vmax$ a successor attaining
$\max\bigl(w^\ast(v^{(0)}),w^\ast(v^{(1)})\bigr)$ and let $\tau^\ast$
choose at each $v\in\Vmin$ a successor attaining the corresponding
minimum. Then
\[
  w^\ast\;=\;\max_\sigma\min_\tau v_{\sigma,\tau}\;=\;\min_\tau\max_\sigma v_{\sigma,\tau}\;=\;\val ,
\]
the extrema being over positional strategies and attained at
$(\sigma^\ast,\tau^\ast)$; and $y_\sigma=\min_\tau v_{\sigma,\tau}=\val_\sigma$
for every $\sigma$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By the choice of $\sigma^\ast,\tau^\ast$ we have
$T_{\sigma^\ast,\tau^\ast}w^\ast=Tw^\ast=w^\ast$, so $w^\ast$ is a fixed
point of $T_{\sigma^\ast,\tau^\ast}$, whence
$w^\ast=v_{\sigma^\ast,\tau^\ast}$ by \Cref{thm:contraction}.

Fix any positional $\tau$. At $v\in\Vmin$ we have
$(T_{\sigma^\ast,\tau}w^\ast)(v)=w^\ast(\tau(v))\ge\min\bigl(w^\ast(v^{(0)}),w^\ast(v^{(1)})\bigr)=w^\ast(v)$,
and at every other vertex $T_{\sigma^\ast,\tau}$ agrees with
$T_{\sigma^\ast,\tau^\ast}$, so $T_{\sigma^\ast,\tau}w^\ast\ge w^\ast$.
By monotonicity the iterates $T_{\sigma^\ast,\tau}^{k}w^\ast$ are
nondecreasing, and by \Cref{thm:contraction} they converge to
$v_{\sigma^\ast,\tau}$; hence $v_{\sigma^\ast,\tau}\ge w^\ast$. As $\tau$
was arbitrary, $\min_\tau v_{\sigma^\ast,\tau}\ge w^\ast$ and therefore
$\max_\sigma\min_\tau v_{\sigma,\tau}\ge w^\ast$. Symmetrically, for
every $\sigma$ we get $T_{\sigma,\tau^\ast}w^\ast\le w^\ast$ and hence
$v_{\sigma,\tau^\ast}\le w^\ast$, so
$\min_\tau\max_\sigma v_{\sigma,\tau}\le w^\ast$. Since
$\max_\sigma\min_\tau\le\min_\tau\max_\sigma$ always, all three
quantities coincide with $w^\ast$, and the extrema are attained at
$(\sigma^\ast,\tau^\ast)$.

The statement for $y_\sigma$ is the same argument with Max's choices
frozen: let $\tau_\sigma$ be greedy for $y_\sigma$ at $\Vmin$; then
$T_{\sigma,\tau_\sigma}y_\sigma=T_\sigma y_\sigma=y_\sigma$ gives
$y_\sigma=v_{\sigma,\tau_\sigma}$, and $T_{\sigma,\tau}y_\sigma\ge y_\sigma$
for every $\tau$ gives $v_{\sigma,\tau}\ge y_\sigma$; hence
$y_\sigma=\min_\tau v_{\sigma,\tau}$, the minimum being attained by the
single positional strategy $\tau_\sigma$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Two-sided comparison]\label{cor:comparison}
Let $G$ be stopping and $\sigma$ a Max strategy. If $x\in\mathcal{X}$
satisfies $x\le T_\sigma x$ then $x\le\val_\sigma$; if
$T_\sigma x\le x$ then $\val_\sigma\le x$. The same holds for $T$ and
$\val$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By \Cref{thm:contraction} and \Cref{thm:stopping-determinacy},
$\val_\sigma$ is the unique fixed point of $T_\sigma$ and every orbit of
$T_\sigma$ converges to it. If $x\le T_\sigma x$ then by monotonicity the
sequence $T_\sigma^{k}x$ is nondecreasing, and it converges to
$\val_\sigma$; hence $x\le\val_\sigma$. If $T_\sigma x\le x$ then
$T_\sigma^{k}x$ is nonincreasing and converges to the same limit, so
$\val_\sigma\le x$. The argument for $T$ and $\val$ is identical.
\end{proof}

\Cref{cor:comparison} is exactly the statement that fails in general
SSGs, by \Cref{rem:no-postfixed}. It is the engine of every switching
argument below.

\begin{remark}[More than two terminals]\label{rem:terminals}
Several later constructions (\Cref{def:payoff}, \Cref{def:freeze}, the
proof of \Cref{thm:trans-complete}) apply the results of this section
and of \Cref{sec:structure} to games in which, besides $t_0$ and $t_1$,
further vertices have been declared terminal with constant payoffs in
$[0,1]$. Nothing changes: \Cref{lem:absorption}, \Cref{thm:contraction},
\Cref{thm:stopping-determinacy}, \Cref{cor:comparison},
\Cref{lem:local-global} and \Cref{thm:opt-subcube} use of the sinks only
that they are absorbing and pay constants, never the values $0$ and $1$
(the two-terminal case with arbitrary payoffs $\alpha<\beta$ is spelled
out in \Cref{lem:gen-comparison}), and such a game is stopping in the
same sense --- every positional pair is absorbed almost surely ---
exactly when, terminals counting as sinks, the only trap in the sense of
\Cref{lem:trapchar} is empty. We use those results in this generality
without further comment.
\end{remark}

\section{The quantitative stopping transformation}\label{sec:transform}

The construction of this section is Condon's: her stopping transformation
damps every edge by a small leak, so that the game becomes stopping while
the values move by less than the separation the denominator bound affords.
What is added here is the bookkeeping --- every constant computed, and the
moved threshold tracked, since after the transformation it is no longer
$\tfrac12$.

\begin{definition}[Damping gadget]\label{def:damping}
Let $G$ be an SSG and $m\ge1$ an integer. The game $G_m$ is obtained by
replacing each edge $u\to w$ of $G$ by a fresh chain of $m$ average
vertices $g_1,\dots,g_m$, where $u$'s edge now leads to $g_1$,
\[
  g_i \longrightarrow \bigl\{w,\;g_{i+1}\bigr\}\quad (1\le i<m),
  \qquad
  g_m \longrightarrow \bigl\{w,\;t_0\bigr\},
\]
each with probability $\tfrac12$. The vertex sets $\Vmax$ and $\Vmin$,
and hence both players' strategy spaces, are unchanged.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[The gadget is a damped edge]\label{lem:gadget}
In $G_m$, a token entering the chain replacing $u\to w$ arrives at $w$
with probability $1-\beta$ and at $t_0$ with probability $\beta$, where
$\beta:=2^{-m}$. The game $G_m$ is a stopping SSG with
$|V(G_m)| = N + 2m(N-2) < (2m+1)N$ vertices.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
From $g_1$ the token reaches $w$ at step $i$ with probability $2^{-i}$
for $1\le i\le m$, and otherwise reaches $t_0$; summing,
$\sum_{i=1}^{m}2^{-i}=1-2^{-m}$. Every non-sink vertex of $G$ has two
outgoing edges, so exactly $2(N-2)$ chains are inserted. Stopping holds
because every chain leads to $t_0$ with positive probability at its last
vertex, so from every vertex, under every strategy pair, a sink is
reachable.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The damped family as a path]\label{def:discount-path}
For an SSG $G$ with non-sink set $V^{\circ}$, $n:=|V^{\circ}|$, and
$\varrho\in[0,1]$ let $G_{\varrho}$ be the game in which every step out of
a non-sink survives with probability $\varrho$ and is diverted to $t_0$
otherwise; its operator is $T_{\varrho}x:=\varrho\,Tx$, a
$\varrho$-contraction for $\varrho<1$ with fixed point $w_{\varrho}$, and a
positional pair $\pi$ with substochastic transition matrix $Q_\pi$ on
$V^{\circ}$ and one-step vector $b_\pi$ into $t_1$ has value
$y^{\pi}_{\varrho}=\varrho(I-\varrho Q_\pi)^{-1}b_\pi$. Put
$\Opt(\varrho):=\{\pi:T_{\varrho}y^{\pi}_{\varrho}=y^{\pi}_{\varrho}\}$. The
\emph{stopping-probability path} of $G$ is $\varrho\mapsto\Opt(\varrho)$ on
$(0,1)$; a \emph{breakpoint} is a $\varrho$ at which $\Opt$ is constant on
no neighbourhood, and $B(G)$ is the number of breakpoints.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:discount-path}
Nothing new is built: at $\varrho=1-2^{-m}$ the values of $G_m$ on $V(G)$
are $w_{\varrho}$, every vertex of $G$ acquiring the factor $1-2^{-m}$ by
\Cref{lem:gadget} (the round-$17$ parametric-path route, checked exactly on
$120$ random games). What is new is to read $\varrho$ as a parameter. The
structure is \Cref{thm:opt-subcube}'s, transported: for $\varrho<1$ one has
$\det(I-\varrho Q_\pi)>0$, so
$N^{\pi}_v:=\det(I-\varrho Q_\pi)\,y^{\pi}_{\varrho}(v)$ is a polynomial in
$\varrho$ of degree at most $n$; $\pi\in\Opt(\varrho)$ iff
$N^{\pi}_v\ge\varrho N^{\pi}_{v^{\bar\pi(v)}}$ at every $v\in\Vmax$ and
$\le$ at every $v\in\Vmin$; for $0<\varrho<1$, $\Opt(\varrho)$ is the
nonempty subcube of the pairs greedy for $w_{\varrho}$ (at $\varrho=0$ every
pair is optimal, $w_0=0$); and, a nonzero polynomial of degree at most
$n+1$ having at most $n+1$ roots, $B(G)\le(n+1)|C|2^{|C|}$, a bound nowhere
near attained below. Moreover $\varrho\mapsto w_{\varrho}$ is nondecreasing
with $w_{\varrho}\uparrow\val$ as $\varrho\uparrow1$, $\val$ the least fixed
point of \Cref{thm:lfp-general} ($T$ preserves nonnegativity, so
$T_{\varrho'}w_{\varrho}\ge w_{\varrho}$ for $\varrho'\ge\varrho$, and
$T_{\varrho}\val\le\val$); hence a pair optimal for every $\varrho$ near $1$
is an optimal pair of $G$ itself, stopping or not --- finding it is
target-equivalent and is no algorithm. The path is not the bias homotopy of
\Cref{sec:bias} in another parametrisation, since there the parameter moves
the right-hand side of the complementarity problem and here the matrix
(\Cref{cor:two-homotopies}); and it is exponentially long
(\Cref{prop:discount-fold}).
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Dyadic first-passage rows are cheap]\label{lem:dyadic-row}
Let $r_1,\dots,r_\ell\ge0$ be dyadic rationals of denominator $2^{d}$ with
$\sum_jr_j\le1$. There is a gadget of at most $\ell d$ fresh average
vertices with a designated entry vertex (the target itself when the row
is a unit vector, in which case the gadget is empty) such that a token entering it
reaches a designated target $w_j$ with probability exactly $r_j$ for each
$j$, and $t_0$ with the remaining probability, meeting no other vertex on
the way. Consequently a harmonic normal form on the controlled set $C$
whose rows are dyadic of denominator $2^{d}$ and each of which leaks to
$t_0$ is the normal form of a stopping SSG on $|C|+2+O(|C|^{2}d)$
vertices.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Put $K_0:=0$ and $K_j:=2^{d}(r_1+\dots+r_j)$, integers with
$K_\ell\le2^{d}$, and assign the leaf $i\in\{0,\dots,2^{d}-1\}$ of the
complete binary tree of depth $d$ to $w_j$ when $K_{j-1}\le i<K_j$ and to
$t_0$ when $i\ge K_\ell$. With a fair coin at every internal node the
entry reaches a uniformly random leaf, hence $w_j$ with probability
$(K_j-K_{j-1})/2^{d}=r_j$. An internal node all of whose leaves are
assigned to one target may be deleted and its incoming edge redirected to
that target, which changes no probability; a node survives only if its
leaf interval straddles one of the $\ell$ boundaries $K_1,\dots,K_\ell$,
and at each depth the intervals are disjoint, so at most $\ell$ nodes
survive per depth. For the consequence, replace each action of each
controlled vertex by such a gadget; the average part between controlled
vertices is then exactly the gadgets, so the first-passage laws are the
given rows. Stopping: a gadget entry whose row leaks has a leaf assigned to
$t_0$ below it, so by \Cref{lem:trapchar} no gadget vertex lies in a trap
--- a trap contains both successors of each of its average vertices ---
and then no controlled vertex does either, since both of its successors
are gadget entries; the only trap is empty.
\end{proof}


\begin{lemma}[Denominator bound]\label{lem:denominator}
Let $H$ be an SSG with $r:=|\Vavg|$ average vertices and let
$(\sigma,\tau)$ be a positional pair. Every coordinate of
$v_{\sigma,\tau}$ is a rational number whose denominator divides a
positive integer bounded by $8^{r/2}$. The same bound applies to $\val$,
since by \Cref{thm:determinacy} $\val=v_{\sigma^\ast,\tau^\ast}$ for a
positional pair. In particular the bound is independent of the number of
Max and Min vertices, and for $r=0$ all values lie in $\{0,1\}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
In the chain $M_{\sigma,\tau}$ every vertex outside
$\Vavg\cup\{t_0,t_1\}$ has a single successor. For a vertex $u$ define
$D(u)$ by following those deterministic steps from $u$ until an element
of $\Vavg\cup\{t_0,t_1\}$ is reached, where we set $D(u)=u$ if $u$
already lies in that set; if the deterministic walk instead enters a
cycle disjoint from $\Vavg\cup\{t_0,t_1\}$, the play never reaches $t_1$
and we put $D(u)=\bot$. Writing $\mathcal{V}(t_1)=1$,
$\mathcal{V}(t_0)=\mathcal{V}(\bot)=0$ and
$\mathcal{V}(w)=v_{\sigma,\tau}(w)$ for $w\in\Vavg$, the strong Markov
property gives $v_{\sigma,\tau}(u)=\mathcal{V}(D(u))$ for every $u$, and
for $u\in\Vavg$ with successors $u^{(0)},u^{(1)}$,
\[
  2\,v_{\sigma,\tau}(u)\;=\;\mathcal{V}\bigl(D(u^{(0)})\bigr)+\mathcal{V}\bigl(D(u^{(1)})\bigr).
\]
Thus the restriction of $v_{\sigma,\tau}$ to $\Vavg$ satisfies a linear
system $Ax=c$ of size at most $r$, where $c$ has entries in
$\{0,1,2\}$ and $A$ has integer entries: the row of $u$ carries
$2-\mu_u(u)$ on the diagonal and $-\mu_u(w)$ in column $w\ne u$, with
$\mu_u(w):=|\{i: D(u^{(i)})=w\}|\in\{0,1,2\}$ and
$\sum_{w}\mu_u(w)\le2$. Every row therefore has Euclidean norm at most
$\sqrt{8}$, the extreme case being $\mu_u(w)=2$ for a single $w\ne u$.

Restrict the system to the set $U\subseteq\Vavg$ of average vertices from
which $t_1$ is reachable in $M_{\sigma,\tau}$; off $U$ the value is $0$,
so those coordinates may be deleted, and the resulting matrix $A_U$ is
nonsingular (from every vertex of $U$ the chain leaves $U$ with positive
probability, so the corresponding substochastic matrix has spectral
radius below $1$). Hadamard's inequality gives
$|\det A_U|\le 8^{|U|/2}\le 8^{r/2}$, and by Cramer's rule every
coordinate of $x$ is an integer divided by $\det A_U$. Since every other
value equals some $\mathcal{V}(D(u))\in\{0,1\}\cup\{x_w\}$, the whole
vector has denominators dividing $\det A_U$. If $r=0$ every value is
$\mathcal{V}(D(u))\in\{0,1\}$.
\end{proof}

The Hadamard estimate above is not tight: the matrix built in that proof
is not an arbitrary matrix of bounded row norm but a substochastic one,
and for such matrices the determinant factorises probabilistically.

\begin{lemma}[Watched-chain factorisation]\label{lem:watched}
Let $S$ be a nonnegative $m\times m$ matrix with every row sum at most
$1$ and $\rho(S)<1$. Then $\det(I-S)=\prod_{k=1}^{m}(1-s_k)$ with every
$s_k\in[0,1)$; in particular $0<\det(I-S)\le1$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Induction on $m$. Put $s_1:=S_{11}$. If $s_1=1$ then, the first row summing
to at most $1$, all other entries of that row vanish, so $e_1^{\mathsf T}$
is a left eigenvector of $S$ with eigenvalue $1$ and $\rho(S)=1$,
contrary to hypothesis; hence $s_1<1$. Schur complementation at the
$(1,1)$ entry gives $\det(I-S)=(1-s_1)\det(I-S^{(1)})$ where
$S^{(1)}_{ij}=S_{ij}+S_{i1}S_{1j}/(1-s_1)$ for $i,j\ge2$. That matrix is
nonnegative, and its $i$-th row sum is
\[
  \sum_{j\ge2}S_{ij}+S_{i1}\frac{\sum_{j\ge2}S_{1j}}{1-s_1}
  \;\le\;\sum_{j\ge2}S_{ij}+S_{i1}\;\le\;1,
\]
using $\sum_{j\ge2}S_{1j}\le1-s_1$. A nonnegative matrix with row sums at
most $1$ and spectral radius $1$ has $1$ as an eigenvalue by
Perron--Frobenius and hence singular $I-S$; since $\det(I-S)\ne0$ forces
$\det(I-S^{(1)})\ne0$, we get $\rho(S^{(1)})<1$ and the induction applies.
(Probabilistically $1-s_k$ is the one-step escape probability of the chain
watched on $\{k,\dots,m\}$; the watched chain is Meyer's stochastic
complement, and the factorisation is the standard censored-chain identity,
reproved here to keep the constants explicit.)
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Denominator bound, sharp form]\label{lem:denominator-sharp}
In the situation of \Cref{lem:denominator}, $\det A_U\le2^{|U|}\le2^{r}$.
Hence every coordinate of $v_{\sigma,\tau}$, and of $\val$, is a rational
whose denominator divides a positive integer at most $2^{r}$, and the
bound is attained --- on the ladder $L_n$ of \Cref{def:ladder} the value
at $w_1$ has denominator exactly $2^{n}=2^{r}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The proof of \Cref{lem:denominator} exhibits $A_U=2I-M$ with $M$
nonnegative, integral, and of row sums at most $2$, and shows $A_U$
nonsingular. Write $A_U=2(I-S)$ with $S:=M/2$, which is nonnegative with
row sums at most $1$; nonsingularity of $A_U$ gives $\rho(S)<1$. By
\Cref{lem:watched}, $\det A_U=2^{|U|}\det(I-S)\le2^{|U|}$. Cramer's rule,
exactly as before, transfers the bound to the denominators. Sharpness is
the back-substitution $W_i=\tfrac12(V_{i+1}+W_{i+1})$ of
\Cref{thm:ladder} with $V_{n+1}=0$, $W_{n+1}=1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Since $2^{r}\le8^{r/2}$, every constant derived below from
\Cref{lem:denominator} remains valid, and each improves if
\Cref{lem:denominator-sharp} is used instead: in
\Cref{thm:stopping-transform} one may take $D:=2^{a}$ and
$m:=\lceil 2a+\log_2 N\rceil+3$. We keep the weaker constant in the
statements so that the quantitative claims can be checked against the
elementary argument alone.
\end{remark}


\begin{theorem}[Exact reduction to a stopping game]\label{thm:stopping-transform}
Let $G$ be an SSG on $N\ge3$ vertices with $a=|\Vavg|$ average vertices
and start vertex $v_0$, put $D:=2^{\lceil 3a/2\rceil}$ --- a power of two,
and by \Cref{lem:denominator} an upper bound on the denominator of
$\val_G(v_0)$, since $8^{a/2}=2^{3a/2}\le D$ --- and choose
\[
  m \;:=\; \bigl\lceil\, \tfrac52 a + \log_2 N \,\bigr\rceil + 3 .
\]
Then $G_m$ is a stopping SSG with $O\bigl(N(a+\log N)\bigr)=O(N^2)$ vertices, computable in time
polynomial in $N$, and
\[
  \val_G(v_0)\;\ge\;\tfrac12
  \qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
  \val_{G_m}(v_0)\;>\;\tfrac12-\tfrac{1}{4D}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $\beta=2^{-m}$. \emph{Step 1: $\val_{G_m}\le\val_G$.} Fix any pair
$(\sigma,\tau)$. Couple the two chains by letting them make identical
choices at $\Vmax\cup\Vmin\cup\Vavg$ vertices; the chain in $G_m$
additionally has, at each traversal of an edge, probability $\beta$ of
being diverted to $t_0$. Under this coupling every play of $G_m$ that
reaches $t_1$ corresponds to a play of $G$ that reaches $t_1$, so
$v^{G_m}_{\sigma,\tau}(v_0)\le v^{G}_{\sigma,\tau}(v_0)$; taking
$\max_\sigma\min_\tau$ preserves the inequality.

\emph{Step 2: $\val_G-\val_{G_m}\le\beta N2^{a}$.} Let $\sigma^\ast$ be
optimal for Max in $G$ and let $\tau$ be arbitrary. In the chain
$M_{\sigma^\ast,\tau}$ of $G$, call a vertex \emph{live} if $t_1$ is
reachable from it, and let $T_{\mathrm{res}}$ be the first time the token
reaches $t_1$ or leaves the live set. From every live vertex there is a
simple path to $t_1$, of length at most $N-1$, and by the argument of
\Cref{lem:absorption} it is followed with probability at least $2^{-a}$;
hence $\Pr[T_{\mathrm{res}}>jN]\le(1-2^{-a})^{j}$ and
$\mathbb{E}[T_{\mathrm{res}}]\le N2^{a}$. Under the coupling of Step 1, a
play of $G_m$ fails to reach $t_1$ only if the corresponding play of $G$
fails to, or if a diversion occurs before time $T_{\mathrm{res}}$. By a
union bound the latter has probability at most
$\beta\,\mathbb{E}[T_{\mathrm{res}}]\le\beta N2^{a}$. Therefore
$v^{G_m}_{\sigma^\ast,\tau}(v_0)\ge v^{G}_{\sigma^\ast,\tau}(v_0)-\beta N2^{a}$
for every $\tau$, and taking $\min_\tau$ and then the maximum over Max
strategies gives $\val_{G_m}(v_0)\ge\val_G(v_0)-\beta N2^{a}$.

\emph{Step 3: separation.} By \Cref{lem:denominator}, $\val_G(v_0)$ is a
rational with denominator at most $D$. If $\val_G(v_0)<\tfrac12$ then
$\tfrac12-\val_G(v_0)\ge\tfrac{1}{2D}$, so by Step 1
$\val_{G_m}(v_0)\le\tfrac12-\tfrac1{2D}<\tfrac12-\tfrac1{4D}$. If
$\val_G(v_0)\ge\tfrac12$ then by Step 2
$\val_{G_m}(v_0)\ge\tfrac12-\beta N2^{a}$, and the choice of $m$ gives
$\beta N 2^{a}=2^{-m}N2^{a}<\tfrac{1}{4D}$: indeed
$2^{-m}N2^{a}<2^{-2}2^{-\lceil 3a/2\rceil}$ is equivalent to
$m>\lceil\tfrac32a\rceil+a+\log_2 N+2$, and since
$\lceil\tfrac32a\rceil\le\tfrac32a+\tfrac12$ the right-hand side is at most
$\tfrac52a+\log_2N+\tfrac52$, which $m=\lceil \tfrac52a+\log_2N\rceil+3$
exceeds. Hence
$\val_{G_m}(v_0)>\tfrac12-\tfrac1{4D}$. The two cases are exclusive and
exhaustive, proving the equivalence. Finally $|V(G_m)|<(2m+1)N=O\bigl(N(a+\log N)\bigr)$ by
\Cref{lem:gadget}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
\Cref{thm:stopping-transform} is what licenses us to assume that the input
game is stopping. The cost is not zero and must be tracked in two ways.
First, the threshold moves: the transformed game is compared against
$\tfrac12-\tfrac1{4D}$, not against $\tfrac12$, and forgetting this gives
wrong answers (see the proof of \Cref{thm:compare-equivalence}). Second,
the gadget inserts $\Theta(a+\log N)$ average vertices in front of every
edge, so any bound whose parameter is $|\Vavg|$ --- such as
\Cref{thm:few-avg} --- must be proved before the transformation, not
after. Every statement in
\Cref{sec:structure,sec:refutations} is therefore stated for stopping
games without loss of generality for the purposes of \Cref{prob:main}.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[The reduction, with the threshold restored]\label{cor:stopping-reduction}
From an arbitrary SSG $G$ with start vertex $v_0$ one constructs in
polynomial time a stopping SSG $G'$ with a start vertex $s$ and
$|V(G')|=O(N(a+\log N))$ such that $\val_G(v_0)\ge\tfrac12$ if and only
if $\val_{G'}(s)\ge\tfrac12$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Let $G_m$ and $D=2^{\lceil3a/2\rceil}$ be as in \Cref{thm:stopping-transform}
and put $\theta:=\tfrac12-\tfrac3{8D}$, so that $1-\theta=\tfrac12+\tfrac3{8D}$
is a dyadic rational with $k:=\lceil3a/2\rceil+3$ binary digits
$b_1\cdots b_k$. Adjoin a chain of $k$ fresh average vertices
$c_1,\dots,c_k$ with $c_i\to(t_{b_i},c_{i+1})$ and $c_{k+1}:=t_0$, so that
$\val(c_i)=\tfrac12(b_i+\val(c_{i+1}))$ and $\val(c_1)=1-\theta$ exactly, and
a fresh average start $s\to(v_0,c_1)$; call the result $G'$. It is
stopping: a trap must contain both successors of every average vertex it
contains, and neither $G_m$ nor the chain, whose every vertex has a sink
successor, offers one. Then
$\val_{G'}(s)=\tfrac12(\val_{G_m}(v_0)+1-\theta)\ge\tfrac12$ if and only if
$\val_{G_m}(v_0)\ge\theta$. By the two cases in the proof of
\Cref{thm:stopping-transform}, $\val_G(v_0)\ge\tfrac12$ gives
$\val_{G_m}(v_0)>\tfrac12-\tfrac2{8D}>\theta$, and $\val_G(v_0)<\tfrac12$
gives $\val_{G_m}(v_0)\le\tfrac12-\tfrac4{8D}<\theta$; so the two
threshold questions agree. The size is that of the theorem plus $k+1=O(a)$.
\end{proof}

\section{A polynomial special case}\label{sec:special}

The sharpened escape estimate has an algorithmic consequence. It is
modest, but it is a genuine positive result, it isolates $|\Vavg|$ rather
than $N$ as the parameter controlling value iteration, and --- unlike
most of what follows --- it needs no stopping hypothesis, so it does not
pay the price of \Cref{thm:stopping-transform} (which inflates $|\Vavg|$
to $\Theta(N^2)$).

\begin{lemma}[Forced descent]\label{lem:descent}
Let $G$ be an arbitrary SSG, $a=|\Vavg|$, and let $\sigma^\ast$ be an
optimal positional Max strategy (\Cref{thm:determinacy}). Put
$C_0:=\{t_1\}$ and
\[
  C_{j+1}:=C_j\cup\bigl\{v\in\Vavg:\{v^{(0)},v^{(1)}\}\cap C_j\ne\emptyset\bigr\}
  \cup\bigl\{v\in\Vmax:\sigma^\ast(v)\in C_j\bigr\}
  \cup\bigl\{v\in\Vmin:\{v^{(0)},v^{(1)}\}\subseteq C_j\bigr\},
\]
and $C_\infty:=C_{N}$. Then
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item $C_\infty=\{v:\val(v)>0\}$;
\item for every $v\in C_\infty$ and \emph{every} Min strategy $\pi$ ---
  positional or history-dependent, deterministic or randomised --- the
  play from $v$ under $(\sigma^\ast,\pi)$ reaches $t_1$ within $N$ steps
  with probability at least $2^{-a}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(b) Write $\ell(v)$ for the least $j$ with $v\in C_j$, and
$A(v):=|\{w\in\Vavg:\ell(w)\le\ell(v)\}|\le a$. We show by induction on
$\ell(v)$ that $t_1$ is reached within $\ell(v)\le N$ steps with
probability at least $2^{-A(v)}$, whatever $\pi$ does. For $\ell(v)=0$
the vertex is $t_1$. Let $\ell(v)=j+1$. If $v\in\Vmax$ then
$\sigma^\ast(v)\in C_j$, and Max moves there with probability $1$. If
$v\in\Vmin$ then \emph{both} successors lie in $C_j$, so whatever $\pi$
does --- at any time, after any history --- the token moves to a vertex
of level at most $j$. If $v\in\Vavg$ then at least one successor lies in
$C_j$ and is taken with probability $\tfrac12$; here $v$ is itself an
average vertex of level $j+1$, so it is counted in $A(v)$ but not in
$A(w)$ for the successor $w$, giving $A(v)\ge A(w)+1$ and hence
$\tfrac12\cdot2^{-A(w)}\ge2^{-A(v)}$. In every case the token descends to
a strictly smaller level, so it reaches $t_1$ within $\ell(v)$ steps.

(a) ($\supseteq$) By (b), $\val(v)\ge\val_{\sigma^\ast}(v)\ge2^{-a}>0$ for
$v\in C_\infty$. ($\subseteq$) Let $S:=V\setminus C_\infty$; then
$t_1\notin S$, every $v\in S\cap\Vmax$ has $\sigma^\ast(v)\in S$, every
$v\in S\cap\Vavg$ has both successors in $S$, and every $v\in S\cap\Vmin$
has at least one successor in $S$ --- in each case because the opposite
would place $v$ in $C_\infty$. Let Min play, inside $S$, a successor in
$S$. Then from any $v\in S$ the token never leaves $S$, hence never
reaches $t_1$, so $\val(v)\le\val_{\sigma^\ast}(v)=0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:few-avg}
Every SSG on $N$ vertices with $a=|\Vavg|$ average vertices, stopping or
not, can be decided --- and its exact value vector computed --- in time
$\mathrm{poly}(N)\cdot 2^{a}$. In particular SSGs with $a=O(\log N)$
average vertices are decidable in polynomial time, and SSGs with no
average vertices at all in time $\mathrm{poly}(N)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Fix an optimal positional $\sigma^\ast$ and let $F:=T_{\sigma^\ast}$, the
operator with Max's choices frozen. Put
$u_j:=F^{jN}\bigl(\bbone_{\{t_1\}}\bigr)$, so that $u_j(v)$ is the
infimum, over all Min strategies, of the probability of reaching $t_1$
from $v$ within $jN$ steps; the infimum is attained by backward
induction, and Min may use time-dependent play. Since $T\ge F$ pointwise
and both are monotone,
\[
  \bbone_{\{t_1\}}\ \le\ u_j\ \le\ T^{jN}\bigl(\bbone_{\{t_1\}}\bigr)\ \le\ \val,
\]
the last inequality because reaching $t_1$ within $jN$ steps implies
reaching it at all. So it suffices to bound $\val-u_j$.

Off $C_\infty$ we have $\val=0$ by \Cref{lem:descent}(a) and hence
$u_j=0$ there too. Put $M_j:=\max_{v\in C_\infty}(\val-u_j)(v)$, so
$M_0\le1$. Because $\sigma^\ast$ is optimal, $\val=\val_{\sigma^\ast}$, and
$F\val=\val$ by \Cref{thm:lfp-general}(a) --- this is exactly the
identification of an MDP value with a fixed point of its own operator in
a game carrying no stopping hypothesis, which is why it is quoted here
rather than asserted --- hence $F^{N}\val=\val$. Now $F^{N}(y)(v)$ equals
$\inf_\pi\bigl[\Pr^{\pi}_v[T\le N]+\mathbb{E}^{\pi}_v[y(X_N)\mathbf1\{T>N\}]\bigr]$,
where $T$ is the hitting time of $t_1$; since
$\inf_\pi A(\pi)-\inf_\pi B(\pi)\le\sup_\pi(A(\pi)-B(\pi))$,
\[
  (\val-u_{j+1})(v)\;=\;\bigl(F^{N}\val-F^{N}u_j\bigr)(v)
  \;\le\;\sup_\pi\ \mathbb{E}^{\pi}_v\bigl[(\val-u_j)(X_N)\,\mathbf1\{T>N\}\bigr].
\]
The integrand vanishes off $C_\infty$ and is at most $M_j$ on it, so for
$v\in C_\infty$ the right-hand side is at most
$M_j\cdot\sup_\pi\Pr^{\pi}_v[T>N]\le M_j(1-2^{-a})$ by
\Cref{lem:descent}(b), the supremum being over \emph{all} Min strategies
--- which is exactly what that lemma provides. Hence
$M_{j+1}\le(1-2^{-a})M_j$ and $\val-u_j\le(1-2^{-a})^{j}\le e^{-j2^{-a}}$
everywhere.

Put $D:=2^{\lceil 3a/2\rceil}$, which bounds the denominators of $\val$ by
\Cref{lem:denominator} and \Cref{thm:determinacy}. Choosing
$j:=\bigl\lceil 2^{a}(2+\lceil\tfrac32a\rceil)\ln2\bigr\rceil+1$ makes the
error below $2^{-2-\lceil 3a/2\rceil}=1/(4D)$. If $\val(v_0)<\tfrac12$
then, $\val(v_0)$ being a rational of denominator at most $D$, we have
$\val(v_0)\le\tfrac12-\tfrac1{2D}$ and hence
$T^{jN}(\bbone_{\{t_1\}})(v_0)\le\tfrac12-\tfrac1{2D}$; if
$\val(v_0)\ge\tfrac12$ then
$T^{jN}(\bbone_{\{t_1\}})(v_0)>\tfrac12-\tfrac1{4D}$. So the threshold
$\tfrac12-\tfrac1{4D}$ decides. The exact value vector is recovered by
rounding each coordinate to the unique rational of denominator at most
$D$ within distance $1/(2D^{2})$, which needs accuracy $1/(2D^2)$ ---
double $\lceil\tfrac32a\rceil$ in $j$ --- followed by the
continued-fraction algorithm.

For the running time, iterate $T$ in binary fixed-point arithmetic with
$B$ bits after the point, rounding every intermediate value down.
Rounding down preserves $T^{k}(\bbone_{\{t_1\}})\le\val$ and adds at most
$2^{-B}$ per application of $T$, hence at most $jN2^{-B}$ in all;
$B=\Theta(a+\log N)$ makes this negligible against $1/(4D)$. The number of
applications of $T$ is $jN=O(2^{a}aN)$, each costing $O(N)$ additions,
comparisons and halvings of $B$-bit numbers, for a total of
$\mathrm{poly}(N)\cdot2^{a}$. Note that the algorithm itself iterates $T$
and never needs $\sigma^\ast$, which appears only in the analysis.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The rate is attained]\label{rem:few-avg-tight}
The rate of \Cref{thm:few-avg} cannot be improved, in two senses. Take
first the game with the single non-sink $c\to\{t_1,c\}$, so that $N=3$
and $a=1$. It is stopping --- the only candidate trap $\{c\}$ has the
successor $t_1$ outside it --- with $\val(c)=1$, and value iteration
gives $T^{k}(\bbone_{\{t_1\}})(c)=1-2^{-k}$ by induction, so
\[
  \val(c)-T^{k}\bigl(\bbone_{\{t_1\}}\bigr)(c)\;=\;2^{-k}\;=\;\bigl(1-2^{-a}\bigr)^{k}
\]
for every $k$: each single application of $T$ reduces the error by
exactly the factor $1-2^{-a}$, the escape probability of
\Cref{lem:descent}(b), and no argument charging one coin per step can do
better. \Cref{thm:few-avg} charges that factor to a block of $N$
applications, and on this instance a block gains $2^{-N}=\tfrac18$ rather
than $\tfrac12$. The block rate is attained once the coin is spread over
a cycle: with $\Vmax=\{p_1,\dots,p_{N-3}\}$ and $\Vavg=\{c\}$,
$c\to\{t_1,p_1\}$, $p_i\to\{p_{i+1},p_{i+1}\}$ and $p_{N-3}\to\{c,c\}$,
the token crosses the coin once every $N-2$ steps, and exactly $j+1$
crossings occur within $jN$ steps as long as $2j-1<N-2$, so
\[
  \val(c)-T^{jN}\bigl(\bbone_{\{t_1\}}\bigr)(c)\;=\;2^{-(j+1)}
  \;=\;\tfrac12\bigl(1-2^{-a}\bigr)^{j}\qquad\bigl(1\le j\le\tfrac{N-2}2\bigr),
\]
verified in exact arithmetic for $N=8,12,20,50$ and $j\le4$. The factor
$1-2^{-a}$ per block of $N$ applications is thus attained up to the
constant $\tfrac12$.
\end{remark}


\begin{remark}
\Cref{thm:few-avg} should be read together with \Cref{thm:vi-lower},
which exhibits games where value iteration provably needs
$2^{\Omega(N)}$ steps; there \emph{every} non-sink vertex is an average
vertex, so $a=N-2$ and the two results are consistent and jointly tight
in their dependence on $a$. Note also that routing this theorem through
\Cref{thm:stopping-transform} would destroy it: the damping gadget
inserts $\Theta(N)$ fresh average vertices in front of every edge, raising
$a$ to $\Theta(N^{2})$. It is precisely because \Cref{lem:descent} needs no
stopping hypothesis that the theorem applies to arbitrary games. The
tractability of games with few average vertices is not new: from memory,
unchecked against the sources, Condon observed it for a constant number
of them, and Gimbert and Horn (``Simple stochastic games with few random
vertices are easy to solve'') gave an algorithm of order
$a!\,\mathrm{poly}(N)$; the $2^{a}\mathrm{poly}(N)$ bound above is the
value-iteration version, and is polynomial where $a!$ is only
quasi-polynomial.
\end{remark}

A third tractable regime is delimited not by how many average vertices
there are, but by where they sit.

The exponent $a$ in \Cref{thm:few-avg} counts \emph{all} average vertices,
while the proof of \Cref{lem:descent} loses a factor $\tfrac12$ only at
those average vertices where the descent actually branches, and only along
one path. Counting exactly that gives a strictly smaller exponent, and one
that can be bounded while $a$ is linear in $N$.

\begin{definition}[Escape exponent]\label{def:escape}
Let $\sigma$ be a Max strategy and let $C_j$, $C_\infty$ and
$\ell(v):=\min\{j:v\in C_j\}$ be as in \Cref{lem:descent} with
$\sigma^\ast$ replaced by $\sigma$, and $\ell:=+\infty$ off $C_\infty$. Call $v=p_0,p_1,\dots,p_r=t_1$ a
\emph{descent path} if $\ell$ strictly decreases along it and
$p_{i+1}=\sigma(p_i)$ whenever $p_i\in\Vmax$. Put $\omega(v):=1$ if
$v\in\Vavg$ has \emph{exactly one} successor of strictly smaller level,
and $\omega(v):=0$ otherwise; let
$A_\sigma(v):=\max\bigl\{\sum_{i}\omega(p_i)\bigr\}$ over descent paths
from $v$, and $d(\sigma):=\max_{v\in C_\infty}A_\sigma(v)$. Finally
$d(G):=\min\{d(\sigma^\ast):\sigma^\ast\text{ optimal}\}$. Then
$d(G)\le a$, since a descent path has strictly decreasing levels and so
meets each average vertex at most once.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Refined forced descent]\label{lem:descent-refined}
In the situation of \Cref{lem:descent}, for every $v\in C_\infty$ and
every Min strategy the play from $v$ under $\sigma^\ast$ reaches $t_1$
