within $\ell(v)\le N$ steps with probability at least
$2^{-A_{\sigma^\ast}(v)}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Induction on $\ell(v)$; the case $\ell(v)=0$ is $v=t_1$. Let
$\ell(v)=j+1$. If $v\in\Vmax$ the token moves to $w:=\sigma^\ast(v)\in C_j$
with probability $1$, and $\omega(v)=0$ while prefixing $v$ to a
maximising descent path from $w$ is again one, so
$A_{\sigma^\ast}(v)\ge A_{\sigma^\ast}(w)$. If $v\in\Vmin$ then both
successors lie in $C_j$, so whatever the adversary does the token moves to
some $w$ with $\ell(w)\le j$, and again $\omega(v)=0$ and
$A_{\sigma^\ast}(v)\ge A_{\sigma^\ast}(w)$. If $v\in\Vavg$ has both
successors of smaller level then $\omega(v)=0$, the token descends with
probability $1$, and $A_{\sigma^\ast}(v)$ dominates both. If exactly one
successor $w$ has smaller level then $\omega(v)=1$,
$A_{\sigma^\ast}(v)\ge A_{\sigma^\ast}(w)+1$, and the token moves to $w$
with probability $\tfrac12$, giving at least
$\tfrac12\cdot2^{-A_{\sigma^\ast}(w)}\ge2^{-A_{\sigma^\ast}(v)}$. In every
case $\ell$ strictly decreases, so $t_1$ is reached within $\ell(v)$ steps.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[A sound and complete polynomial certificate]\label{lem:certificate}
Let $G$ be an arbitrary SSG and $x\in\mathcal X$. Call a successor of
$v\in\Vmax$ \emph{greedy} for $x$ if it attains
$\max(x(v^{(0)}),x(v^{(1)}))$; let $D_0:=\{t_1\}$ and
\[
  D_{j+1}:=D_j\cup\{v\in\Vavg:\text{some successor in }D_j\}
  \cup\{v\in\Vmax:\text{some greedy successor in }D_j\}
  \cup\{v\in\Vmin:\text{both successors in }D_j\},
\]
and let $\sigma_x$ choose at every $v\in\Vmax\cap D_\infty$ a greedy
successor of strictly smaller $D$-level, and any greedy successor
elsewhere. Then $x=\val$ if and only if $Tx=x$ and $\val_{\sigma_x}=x$,
and both tests are polynomial.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
($\Leftarrow$) $Tx=x$ gives $\val\le x$ because $\val=\lfp(T)$
(\Cref{thm:lfp-general}), and $\val\ge\val_{\sigma_x}=x$ always.
($\Rightarrow$) $T\val=\val$ is \Cref{thm:lfp-general}. Every optimal
$\sigma^\ast$ is greedy for $\val$, so $C_j^{\sigma^\ast}\subseteq D_j$
and $D_\infty\supseteq\{\val>0\}$ by \Cref{lem:descent}(a); moreover
$\sigma_x$ being greedy gives $T_{\sigma_x}\val=T\val=\val$, so $\val$
satisfies every equality and inequality of the program of
\Cref{thm:eval-stopfree}. It remains that $\val$ vanishes on
$Z_{\sigma_x}$, which by \Cref{lem:trap} is $\{\val_{\sigma_x}=0\}$; and
the sets $D_j$ satisfy exactly the three closure conditions used in
\Cref{lem:descent-refined} --- both successors at $\Vmin$, at least one at
$\Vavg$, the chosen $\sigma_x(v)$ at $\Vmax$ --- so that proof applies
verbatim and gives $\val_{\sigma_x}(v)\ge2^{-a}>0$ whenever $\val(v)>0$.
Hence $\val$ is feasible for that program, whose unique optimum dominates
every feasible point, so $\val\le\val_{\sigma_x}\le\val$.
Polynomiality: $Tx=x$ costs $O(N)$ operations and $\val_{\sigma_x}$ is the
linear program of \Cref{thm:eval-stopfree}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Value iteration is governed by the escape
exponent]\label{thm:few-escape}
There is a deterministic algorithm that outputs the exact value vector of
an arbitrary SSG, stopping or not, in time
$\mathrm{poly}(N)\,a\,2^{d(G)}\le\mathrm{poly}(N)\,a\,2^{a}$, and that
needs no advance knowledge of $d(G)$. In particular \Cref{prob:main} is
decidable in polynomial time on every family with $d(G)=O(\log N)$. This
contains \Cref{thm:few-avg}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Run the fixed-point iteration $u_j:=T^{jN}(1_{t_1})$ in binary fixed point
with $\Theta(a+\log N)$ bits, rounding down, exactly as in
\Cref{thm:few-avg}; after each block round the iterate to the nearest
rational vector of denominator at most $2^{a}$ by continued fractions and
apply \Cref{lem:certificate}, stopping on acceptance. Soundness is the
easy direction of \Cref{lem:certificate}. For the running time, repeat the
estimate of \Cref{thm:few-avg} with an optimal $\sigma^\ast$ attaining
$d(G)$: writing $M_j:=\max_{C_\infty}(\val-u_j)$,
\Cref{lem:descent-refined} bounds the probability of \emph{not} reaching
$t_1$ within a block by $1-2^{-d(G)}$ against every Min strategy, so
$M_{j+1}\le(1-2^{-d(G)})M_j$ and $\val-u_j\le(1-2^{-d(G)})^{j}$. Two
distinct coordinates of $\val$ differ by at least $4^{-a}$
(\Cref{lem:denominator-sharp}), so after $O(a2^{d(G)})$ blocks the
rounding returns $\val$ exactly and the certificate accepts.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The escape exponent can be $2$ while $a$ is
linear]\label{prop:fk-family}
For $k\ge2$ let $F_k$ have $\Vavg=\{u_1,\dots,u_k,s,e\}$,
$\Vmin=\{y,v_0\}$, $\Vmax=\{x_0,x_1,x_2\}$ and
\[
\begin{gathered}
  u_i\to(t_1,u_{i+1})\ (i<k),\quad u_k\to(t_1,t_0),\quad s\to(u_1,t_0),\quad
  e\to(t_1,y),\quad y\to(s,e),\\
  v_0\to(e,x_0),\quad x_0\to(y,x_1),\quad x_1\to(t_0,x_2),\quad x_2\to(t_0,x_0).
\end{gathered}
\]
Then $N=k+9$, $a=k+2=N-7$, $F_k$ is \emph{not} stopping,
$\val(v_0)=\tfrac12-2^{-(k+1)}$, and $d(F_k)=2$. So
\Cref{thm:few-escape} decides $F_k$ in polynomial time although
\Cref{thm:few-avg} gives only $2^{\Theta(N)}$; and none of
\Cref{thm:avg-acyclic}, \Cref{thm:player-free}, \Cref{thm:one-player} or
\Cref{thm:kacyclic} applies, since the cycle $e\to y\to e$ carries an
average vertex, the cycle $x_0\to x_1\to x_2\to x_0$ carries Max
vertices, $y$ lies on a cycle, and both player sets are non-empty.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Back-substitution gives $\val(u_k)=\tfrac12$ and
$\val(u_i)=\tfrac12(1+\val(u_{i+1}))$, so $\val(u_1)=1-2^{-k}$ and
$\val(s)=\tfrac12\val(u_1)=\tfrac12-2^{-(k+1)}$. Since $\val(e)\ge\tfrac12>\val(s)$,
Min plays $y\to s$, so $\val(y)=\val(s)$ and
$\val(e)=\tfrac12(1+\val(s))$; the least fixed point on the Max cycle gives
$\val(x_i)=\val(y)$, and $\val(v_0)=\min(\val(e),\val(x_0))=\val(s)$.
$F_k$ is not stopping because under $x_j\to x_{j+1}$ the token can
circulate for ever, so the sink attractor of \Cref{def:attractor} omits
$x_0,x_1,x_2$ and \Cref{lem:absorption} fails. For $d$: with the optimal
$\sigma^\ast$ above one computes $\ell(u_i)=1$ and $\omega(u_i)=1$, so
$A(u_i)=1$; $\ell(e)=1$, $\omega(e)=1$, $A(e)=1$; $\ell(s)=2$ with the
unique low successor $u_1$, so $\omega(s)=1$ and $A(s)=2$; then
$A(y)=\max(A(s),A(e))=2$ and $A(x_i)=A(v_0)=2$. All values and all the
non-membership tests were computed exactly for
$k=2,3,4,6,8$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:escape}
The two exponents measure different things. $a$ counts the coin flips the
game contains; $d(G)$ counts the coin flips a \emph{single} forced descent
must survive, and \Cref{prop:fk-family} separates them by
$\Theta(N)$. What \Cref{rem:few-avg-tight} shows is still true and is
about the rate, not the exponent: on the one-vertex game
$c\to\{t_1,c\}$ one has $d=a=1$ and the contraction factor
$1-2^{-1}$ is attained. The refined bound was checked against exhaustive
computation on $1130$ random SSGs for $j=1,2,3$ blocks with no violation,
as was the consequence that every positive value is at least $2^{-d}$,
and the certificate of \Cref{lem:certificate} accepted the true value
vector on $2862$ instances of which $2179$ were not stopping.
\end{remark}

The exponents $a$ and $d(G)$ are combinatorial. There is also a purely
quantitative sufficient condition, certifiable by one linear programme,
which is incomparable with all of the above; it is the one useful thing
the multiplicative view of \Cref{sec:ratio} yields, the eigenvalue itself
being vacuous by \Cref{prop:cw}.

\begin{definition}[Survival operator, escape certificate]\label{def:survival}
Write $V':=V\setminus\{t_0,t_1\}$ and for $x\in[0,\infty)^{V'}$ let
$\hat x$ extend $x$ by $\hat x(t_0)=\hat x(t_1)=0$. The \emph{survival
operator} is
\[
  (Sx)(v):=\begin{cases}
    \max\bigl(\hat x(v^{(0)}),\hat x(v^{(1)})\bigr), & v\in\Vmax\cup\Vmin,\\[2pt]
    \tfrac12\bigl(\hat x(v^{(0)})+\hat x(v^{(1)})\bigr), & v\in\Vavg,
  \end{cases}
\]
the maximum being taken at \emph{every} controlled vertex, Min's as well
as Max's. An \emph{escape certificate} is a pair $(\lambda,x)$ with
$\lambda\in(0,1)$ rational and $x$ a rational vector with $x\ge\bbone$
and $Sx\le\lambda x$; its \emph{conditioning} is
$\kappa(x):=\max_{V'}x/\min_{V'}x$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:survival}
Let $G$ be an SSG.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item For all $y,z$ agreeing with the sink payoffs,
      $|T^ky-T^kz|\le S^k|y-z|$ on $V'$.
\item Let $u_0$ be $\bbone$ on $V'$ and $l_0$ be $0$ on $V'$, both
      carrying the sink payoffs $0$ at $t_0$ and $1$ at $t_1$, and put
      $u_{k+1}:=Tu_k$, $l_{k+1}:=Tl_k$. If $(\lambda,x)$ is an escape
      certificate then $\|u_k-l_k\|_\infty\le\lambda^{k}\kappa(x)$ on
      $V'$. The sink payoffs must be carried: starting from the constants
      $\bbone$ and $0$ the bound fails already at $k=1$, because the
      first application then reads the wrong value at a sink successor.
\item A game carrying an escape certificate is stopping.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) $|\max(a_0,a_1)-\max(b_0,b_1)|\le\max_i|a_i-b_i|$ and the same for
$\min$, while $|\tfrac12\sum a_i-\tfrac12\sum b_i|\le\tfrac12\sum|a_i-b_i|$;
$T$ pins the sinks, so their contribution to the difference is $0$, which
is what $\hat x(t_j)=0$ records. Induct, using that $S$ is monotone.

(b) $u_0$ and $l_0$ agree with the sink payoffs and
$|u_0-l_0|=\bbone\le x/\min x$ on $V'$, so (a) applies and gives
$|u_k-l_k|\le S^k\bbone$. Since $S$ is monotone and positively
homogeneous,
$S^k\bbone\le S^kx/\min x\le\lambda^kx/\min x\le\lambda^k\kappa(x)$.

(c) For any positional pair $(\sigma,\tau)$ and any $v$, the probability
of not reaching a sink within $k$ steps is at most $(S^k\bbone)(v)$,
because at an average vertex $S$ is the exact one-step average and at a
controlled vertex the maximum dominates whichever successor the profile
chooses. By (b) this tends to $0$, so absorption is certain.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The escape class]\label{thm:escape-class}
Let $G$ carry an escape certificate $(\lambda,x)$. Then the algorithm of
\Cref{thm:few-escape} --- iterate $T$ in binary fixed point, round after
each block to the nearest rational vector of denominator at most $2^{a}$,
and stop when \Cref{lem:certificate} accepts --- outputs $\val$ after
\[
  O\!\left(N+\frac{a+\log_2\kappa(x)}{\log_2(1/\lambda)}\right)
\]
iterations of $T$, the rounding and the check being made at the ends of
the blocks of $N$ iterations of \Cref{thm:few-escape}. Consequently \Cref{prob:main} is decidable in
polynomial time on every family of SSGs carrying escape certificates with
$\log_2\kappa(x)=\mathrm{poly}(N)$ and $\log_2(1/\lambda)\ge1/\mathrm{poly}(N)$.
The algorithm uses no knowledge of $\lambda$ or $x$; the certificate
enters only the running-time analysis. Finally, for a fixed rational
$\lambda$ and bound $B$ the existence of a certificate with
$\bbone\le x\le2^{B}\bbone$ is decided by a single linear programme with
$|V'|$ variables and $O(N)$ rows.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
With $u_k,l_k$ as in \Cref{lem:survival}(b) one has
$l_k\le\val\le u_k$ by monotonicity of $T$, and
$u_k-l_k\le\lambda^k\kappa(x)$. Note $l_0=\bbone_{\{t_1\}}$ is exactly
the vector \Cref{thm:few-escape} iterates. Two distinct
coordinates of $\val$ differ by at least $4^{-a}$, since by
\Cref{lem:denominator-sharp} both have denominators dividing an integer
at most $2^{a}$. So once $\lambda^k\kappa(x)<4^{-a}$, which is the stated
number of iterations, the rounding step of \Cref{thm:few-escape} returns
$\val$ exactly and \Cref{lem:certificate} accepts. Correctness on every
input is the soundness half of \Cref{lem:certificate} and does not depend
on the certificate.

For the programme: $Sx\le\lambda x$ is the conjunction of
$\hat x(v^{(i)})\le\lambda x(v)$ for both $i$ at each controlled $v$ and
of $\tfrac12(\hat x(v^{(0)})+\hat x(v^{(1)}))\le\lambda x(v)$ at each
average $v$, because a maximum on the \emph{left} of a $\le$ splits into
a conjunction. With the box $\bbone\le x\le2^{B}\bbone$ this is a
feasibility programme in $|V'|$ variables with at most $2|V'|$ structural
rows.
\end{proof}

The rate a certificate can achieve is bounded below by an ordinary
spectral radius, and this converts every claim of the form ``this family
lies outside the escape class'' --- a statement quantified over all
certificates --- into a computation on the game itself.

\begin{definition}[Selections and the survival rate]\label{def:survival-rate}
A \emph{selection} is a map $\pi$ assigning to each controlled vertex
$v\in\Vmax\cup\Vmin$ one of $v^{(0)},v^{(1)}$. Let $M_\pi$ be the
nonnegative matrix on $V'=V\setminus\{t_0,t_1\}$ whose row at a controlled
$v$ is the indicator of $\pi(v)$, read as the zero row if $\pi(v)$ is a
sink, and whose row at an average $v$ is
$\tfrac12(e_{v^{(0)}}+e_{v^{(1)}})$ with the sink coordinates again
deleted. Put $\lambda^{\ast}(G):=\max_\pi\rho(M_\pi)$, the maximum over the
$2^{|C|}$ selections.
\end{definition}

Each $M_\pi$ is substochastic, and comparing \Cref{def:survival} with
\Cref{def:survival-rate} gives $Sx=\max_\pi M_\pi x$ for every $x\ge0$:
the maximum at a controlled vertex is the choice of $\pi$ there, and the
average rows do not depend on $\pi$ at all.

\begin{lemma}[Every certificate is bounded by every selection]\label{lem:escape-rate}
Let $(\lambda,x)$ be an escape certificate for $G$. Then
$\lambda\ge\rho(M_\pi)$ for \emph{every} selection $\pi$, hence
$\lambda\ge\lambda^{\ast}(G)$. Consequently the bound of
\Cref{thm:escape-class} is never smaller than
$(a+\log_2\kappa(x))/\log_2(1/\lambda^{\ast}(G))$, and a family of games with
$1-\lambda^{\ast}(G)=2^{-\Omega(N)}$ carries no escape certificate with
$\log_2(1/\lambda)\ge1/\mathrm{poly}(N)$: it lies outside the escape
class. Moreover $\lambda^{\ast}(G)<1$ if and only if $G$ is stopping.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Fix $\pi$. From $M_\pi x\le Sx\le\lambda x$ and $x\ge\bbone$: let
$y\ge0$, $y\ne0$, be a left Perron vector of the nonnegative matrix
$M_\pi$, so that $y^{\mathsf T}M_\pi=\rho(M_\pi)\,y^{\mathsf T}$
(Perron--Frobenius needs no irreducibility for this). Then
\[
  \rho(M_\pi)\,y^{\mathsf T}x=y^{\mathsf T}M_\pi x\le\lambda\,y^{\mathsf T}x,
\]
and $y^{\mathsf T}x\ge y^{\mathsf T}\bbone>0$, so $\rho(M_\pi)\le\lambda$.

For the last clause, $M_\pi$ is substochastic, so $\rho(M_\pi)=1$ exactly
when some nonempty $U\subseteq V'$ has every $M_\pi$-row of a vertex of
$U$ supported in $U$ with row sum $1$ (the argmax of a nonnegative Perron
eigenvector, as written out in the proof of \Cref{thm:alphabet-rigid}) --- at a controlled $v$ that says
$\pi(v)\in U$, at an average $v$ that says both successors lie in $U$.
Maximising over $\pi$, such a $U$ exists for some $\pi$ exactly when some
nonempty $U$ has \emph{some} successor in $U$ at every controlled vertex
and both at every average one, which by \Cref{lem:trapchar} is exactly a
nonempty trap. So $\lambda^{\ast}(G)=1$ iff $G$ is not stopping.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:escape-rate}
Three uses and one attribution. First, \Cref{lem:escape-rate} needs only
\emph{one} selection to be exhibited, and it recovers the wedge bound of
\Cref{rem:escape-class} without composing constraints by hand: computing
$\lambda^{\ast}$ over all selections gives
$\lambda^{\ast}(\mathrm{WD}(e,j,m))=0.8846\ldots,0.9497\ldots,0.9768\ldots,0.9889\ldots$
at $\max(e,j)=2,3,4,5$, so $1-\lambda^{\ast}\to0$ geometrically in $\max(e,j)$
while $N$ grows linearly in it. The measurement also shows that
$\lambda^{\ast}(\mathrm{WD}(e,j,m))$ depends on $e$ and $j$ only through
$\max(e,j)$ --- it agrees on $(2,3,5),(3,2,5),(3,3,5)$ and on
$(2,4,6),(4,2,6),(3,4,6),(4,3,6)$ --- and not on $e$ alone.

Second, on the player-free family $G_m$ of \Cref{thm:vi-lower} there is
only one selection and $\lambda^{\ast}(G_m)=1/(2u)$, where $u$ is the root in
$(\tfrac12,1)$ of $2u-1=u^{m}$; so $1-\lambda^{\ast}(G_m)=2^{-m}(1+o(1))$, and
value iteration's exponential lower bound there is visible as a spectral
radius. Third, the last clause of the lemma re-proves
\Cref{lem:survival}(c) and its converse in one line: a game is stopping
exactly when \emph{some} rate below $1$ is certifiable at all.

The converse inequality --- that for every rational $\lambda>\lambda^{\ast}(G)$
an escape certificate with that rate exists --- is true but is not used
here, and it is not elementary: it is the statement that the joint
spectral radius of a family of nonnegative matrices with independent row
uncertainty is attained at a constant selection, which is
Blondel--Nesterov's theorem for rectangular families, and in the language
of nonnegative dynamic programming the stationarity of growth-optimal
policies. With it, $\lambda^{\ast}(G)$ is exactly the infimum of the certifiable rates ---
an infimum, not a minimum: a certificate exists for every rational
$\lambda>\lambda^{\ast}(G)$ but not in general at $\lambda^{\ast}(G)$ itself --- and the
escape class of \Cref{thm:escape-class} is precisely
$\{G:1-\lambda^{\ast}(G)\ge1/\mathrm{poly}(N)\}$, an intrinsic spectral
condition; without it, \Cref{lem:escape-rate} still gives the direction
that every exclusion argument needs.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[The escape class contains none of the
others]\label{prop:escape-family}
For $D\ge3$ let $Z_D$ have $\Vavg=\{s_1,\dots,s_D\}$,
$\Vmax=\{M_2,\dots,M_D\}$, $\Vmin=\{W\}$ and
\[
  s_1\to(t_1,W),\qquad s_j\to(M_j,t_0)\ (2\le j\le D),\qquad
  M_j\to(s_{j-1},t_0),\qquad W\to(s_D,t_1),
\]
with start vertex $s_D$. Then $N=2D+2$, $a=D$, $|\Vmax|=D-1$, $Z_D$ is
stopping, every non-sink is reachable from the start vertex, and all
$2D$ non-sinks lie in a single strongly connected component, which
therefore carries all three colours. Moreover
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item $x:=\bbone$ on $\Vavg$ and $\tfrac32$ on $\Vmax\cup\Vmin$ satisfies
      $Sx\le\tfrac34x$, so $(\tfrac34,x)$ is an escape certificate of
      conditioning $\kappa=\tfrac32$, independent of $D$, and
      \Cref{thm:escape-class} decides $Z_D$ in $O(N)$ iterations;
\item $a=D$, so \Cref{thm:few-avg} gives only $2^{\Theta(N)}$;
\item $d(Z_D)=D$, so \Cref{thm:few-escape} gives only $2^{\Theta(N)}$;
\item the unique non-trivial strongly connected component carries a Max,
      a Min and an average vertex, so $Z_D$ is $k$-acyclic for no colour
      $k$ and \Cref{thm:kacyclic} --- hence also \Cref{thm:avg-acyclic},
      \Cref{thm:player-free} and \Cref{thm:one-player} --- does not
      apply, and neither does any refinement of it that treats the
      strongly connected components separately;
\item the Max-reachability digraph of \Cref{def:maxreach} is a single
      component of size $D-1$, so \Cref{thm:bounded-components} gives
      only $2^{\Theta(N)}$ and $Z_D$ lies in no $K_k$;
\item $|\Vmax|=D-1$, so \Cref{thm:subexp} gives only
      $e^{2\sqrt{D-1}}\mathrm{poly}(N)$;
\item the values are $\val(s_j)=2^{D-j}/(2^{D}-1)$,
      $\val(M_j)=2^{D-j+1}/(2^{D}-1)$ and $\val(W)=1/(2^{D}-1)$, so the
      common denominator is $2^{D}-1=2^{(N-2)/2}-1$ and
      \Cref{thm:few-denominator} gives only $2^{\Theta(N)}$.
\end{enumerate}
Conversely $F_k$ of \Cref{prop:fk-family} carries no escape certificate
for any $\lambda<1$, being non-stopping (\Cref{lem:survival}(c)), while
$d(F_k)=2$. So the escape class is incomparable with the class of
\Cref{thm:few-escape}, and by (ii)--(vii) it is contained in none of the
six; whether it contains the class of \Cref{thm:few-avg} we have not
determined, and it does meet it (\Cref{rem:escape-class}).

The comparison is between \emph{bounds}, not between algorithms. On
$Z_D$ the procedure of \Cref{thm:few-escape} does terminate quickly, as
\Cref{thm:escape-class} says --- it is the same procedure. What items
(ii), (iii), (v) and (vi) record is that the running-time bounds those
theorems supply are exponential there, because the parameters they are
stated in are all $\Theta(N)$ on $Z_D$, whereas the certificate supplies
a polynomial one. A polynomial class in this sense is a set of instances
on which some proved bound is polynomial, and that is how all six should
be read.

The class has a reading that makes it less arbitrary than the inequality
$Sx\le\lambda x$ suggests. Summing \Cref{lem:survival}(b),
\[
  \sum_{k\ge0}(S^{k}\bbone)(v)\ \le\ \frac{\kappa(x)}{1-\lambda}
  \qquad\text{for every }v,
\]
and the left side is the expected time the token survives off the sinks
when \emph{both} players play to survive. So a certificate of polynomial
conditioning and rate is exactly a polynomial bound on that survival time,
and $Z_D$ says the four combinatorial exponents can all be $\Theta(N)$
while it is $O(1)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Every non-sink lies on the cycle
$s_D\to M_D\to s_{D-1}\to\cdots\to s_1\to W\to s_D$, which gives both the
reachability and the single component, and that cycle carries $s_j$,
$M_j$ and $W$. Stopping: every $s_j$ is average with a sink among its
successors, so lies in no trap, and then $M_j$ and $W$ are controlled
with both successors outside one, so \Cref{lem:trapchar} applies.

(i) Under $S$ every controlled vertex is a maximiser and every sink reads
$0$, so the constraints are $x(W)/2\le\lambda x(s_1)$,
$x(M_j)/2\le\lambda x(s_j)$, $x(s_{j-1})\le\lambda x(M_j)$ and
$x(s_D)\le\lambda x(W)$. With $\lambda=\tfrac34$ and the stated $x$ they
read $\tfrac34\le\tfrac34$, $\tfrac34\le\tfrac34$, $1\le\tfrac98$ and
$1\le\tfrac98$. Composing the constraints around the cycle shows they are satisfiable
only for $2\lambda^{2}\ge1$, so $2^{-1/2}$ is the least admissible rate;
rational rates above it are admissible with other vectors --- for
$\lambda=\tfrac{71}{100}$, $x=1$ on $\Vavg$ and $\tfrac{141}{100}$ on the
controlled vertices satisfies every constraint, as
$\tfrac{141}{200}\le\tfrac{71}{100}$ and $1\le\tfrac{10011}{10000}$ ---
and $\tfrac34$ is used for its conditioning $\tfrac32$. (ii), (v) and (vi) are counts.
(iii) is a computation from \Cref{def:escape}, minimised over the
optimal Max strategies. (iv) is the previous paragraph. (vii): once the
values are positive Max prefers $s_{j-1}$ to $t_0$ at $M_j$ and Min prefers
$s_D$ to $t_1$ at $W$, so $\val(s_j)=\tfrac12\val(s_{j-1})$ for $j\ge2$ and
$\val(s_1)=\tfrac12(1+\val(s_D))=\tfrac12(1+2^{1-D}\val(s_1))$, whence
$\val(s_1)=2^{D-1}/(2^{D}-1)$ and the rest follows; the odd denominator
$2^{D}-1$ is coprime to every numerator $2^{i}$. We confirmed the values
exactly for $D=3,\dots,8$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:escape-class}
Three qualifications. First, the condition is quantitative rather than
structural. On a stopping game $S^k\bbone\to0$ --- the converse of
\Cref{lem:survival}(c), absorption being certain --- and
\Cref{thm:contraction} bounds that rate by $(1-2^{-a})^{k/N}$; the
content of \Cref{thm:escape-class} is that the rate be polynomially
bounded below, which the other classes achieve by combinatorial means
and $Z_D$ by none of them. Whether every stopping game whose bracket
contracts quickly admits a certificate attaining that rate we have not
determined; the theorem needs only the direction proved. Second, the theorem is a small one: it changes exactly one estimate in
the running-time proof of \Cref{thm:few-escape}, the per-block absorption
bound $1-2^{-d(G)}$ coming from \Cref{lem:descent-refined}, replacing it
by the certified $\lambda$; the algorithm, its correctness and its
stopping test are unchanged, and \Cref{lem:survival}(a) is the
monotonicity of $T$ read coordinatewise. The content is
\Cref{prop:escape-family}: that this single substitution reaches
instances the combinatorial exponents do not. The
sub-eigenvector inequality $Sx\le\lambda x$ and the accompanying
Collatz--Wielandt vocabulary are classical, and the observation that a
maximum on the left of an inequality splits into a conjunction is what
makes it an ordinary linear programme; what is specific here is the
survival operator, in which \emph{both} players are read as maximisers,
and \Cref{prop:escape-family}. Third, this is the only positive
consequence the multiplicative view yields: by \Cref{prop:cw} the
eigenvalue of the homogenised Shapley operator is $1$ on every instance
and its Collatz--Wielandt bracket bounds nothing, so it is the survival
operator, and not the Shapley operator, that carries the information.

Fourth, and this bounds the significance of the class exactly: no member
of it can be a stalling instance for any of the propagation mechanisms of
\Cref{sec:gap}. By \Cref{thm:slack-vi-upper},
$\Delta_{2k}(x,y)\le u_k(x)-l_k(y)$ with $u_k=T^{k}\mathbf1$ and
$l_k=T^{k}\mathbf0$ from the constants; since $T$ pins the sinks from
the first application, these are for $k\ge1$ the iterates
$u_{k-1},l_{k-1}$ of \Cref{lem:survival}(b) from the sink-carrying
vectors, so a certificate gives $u_k-l_k\le\lambda^{k-1}\kappa$ and
$\Delta_{2k}(x,y)\le w^\ast(x)-w^\ast(y)+2\lambda^{k-1}\kappa$; two
distinct values differ by at least $4^{-a}$, so the own-successor test of
\Cref{rem:own-successor} fires for the plain calculus of
\Cref{def:slack} --- and hence for every mechanism dominating it ---
within the same $O((a+\log_2\kappa)/\log_2(1/\lambda))$ rounds. The
escape class therefore lies entirely on the easy side of every stall
recorded in this document, and cannot reach the frontier. The wedge shows
how far: at $v_i$ the survival dynamics may enter the chain
$a_{i,1},\dots,a_{i,e}$, each of whose vertices returns to $v_i$ with
probability $\tfrac12$, and writing $V:=x(v_i)$, $A_q:=x(a_{i,q})$ the
constraints $\lambda V\ge A_1$ and $2\lambda A_q\ge V+A_{q+1}$ compose
to $A_e\le\bigl(\alpha^\ast+(2\lambda)^{e-1}(\lambda-\alpha^\ast)\bigr)V$
with $\alpha^\ast=1/(2\lambda-1)$; since $A_e\ge V/(2\lambda)>0$ this
forces $(2\lambda)^{e-1}(2\lambda+1)(1-\lambda)<1$, hence
$1-\lambda=O(2^{-e})$ for every admissible $\lambda$ on $WD(e,j,m)$ (the
sharper $1-\lambda<2^{1-e}/3$ does not follow from this inequality alone:
at $e=2$ it admits $\lambda=\tfrac{83}{100}$) and
$\log_2(1/\lambda)=2^{-\Theta(N)}$. Its interest is therefore that it is a
polynomial class incomparable with the class of \Cref{thm:few-escape}
and contained in none of the combinatorial ones
(\Cref{prop:escape-family}) --- not disjoint from them: the games $E_M$
with $\Vmax=\{x_1,\dots,x_M\}$, $x_i\to\{x_{i+1},x_{i+1}\}$,
$x_M\to\{u,u\}$ and the coin $u\to\{t_0,t_1\}$ have $a=1$ and carry the
certificate $(\tfrac12,x)$ with $x(x_i)=2^{M-i+1}$ and $x(u)=1$, so they
lie in this class and in that of \Cref{thm:few-avg} --- and not that it
is close to the open problem.

A word on how the family was found, because the failure is instructive.
A first candidate placed the separating structure --- the cycles carrying
the three colours, the large Max-reachability component --- in a part of
the game the designated start vertex could not reach, leaving a
player-free acyclic chain to decide, which \Cref{thm:avg-acyclic}
settles in linear time. Membership of a polynomial class is a property of
the instance \emph{as posed}, so every such claim must be checked on the
subgame reachable from $v_0$; $Z_D$ is reachable in its entirety, and its
single strongly connected component is what makes it immune to a
component-by-component refinement of \Cref{thm:kacyclic} as well.
\end{remark}



\begin{definition}[Average-jump digraph]\label{def:jump}
For an SSG $G$, let $J(G)$ be the digraph on vertex set $\Vavg$ with an
edge $u\to z$ whenever some path $u=p_0\to p_1\to\cdots\to p_k=z$ in $G$
has $k\ge1$, $z\in\Vavg$ and $p_1,\dots,p_{k-1}\notin\Vavg$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:jump-acyclic}
If no cycle of $G$ contains an average vertex, then $J(G)$ is acyclic.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
A cycle $u_1\to\cdots\to u_k\to u_1$ of $J(G)$ expands, edge by edge,
into a closed walk of $G$ through $u_1\in\Vavg$. A shortest closed walk
of positive length from $u_1$ to itself repeats no vertex --- splitting
at a repetition, including a repetition of $u_1$, would produce a shorter
positive-length closed walk at $u_1$ --- hence is a cycle, and it
contains the average vertex $u_1$, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Deterministic games with terminal payoffs]\label{lem:det-game}
Let $H$ be a game whose non-terminal vertices are partitioned into Max
and Min vertices, each of out-degree two, with a terminal set $Z$
carrying payoffs $c:Z\to[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}$, where a play never reaching
$Z$ pays Max $0$. For $\theta>0$ write
$X_\theta:=\{z\in Z:c(z)\ge\theta\}$ and let $\mathrm{Attr}(X)$ be the set
of vertices from which Max can force a visit to $X$. Then the value of
$H$ at $v$ equals
$\max\bigl(\{0\}\cup\{\theta\in c(Z):v\in\mathrm{Attr}(X_\theta)\}\bigr)$,
computable in time $O(|Z|\cdot|H|)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $v\in\mathrm{Attr}(X_\theta)$ then Max forces a visit to $X_\theta$
within $|H|$ steps and is paid at least $\theta$. If
$v\notin\mathrm{Attr}(X_\theta)$ then the complement of
$\mathrm{Attr}(X_\theta)$ is a trap for Min: every Max vertex outside it
has both successors outside, and every Min vertex outside it has one, so
Min has a positional strategy keeping the play out of $X_\theta$ forever.
The play then either never reaches $Z$, paying $0<\theta$, or reaches
some $z$ with $c(z)<\theta$; either way the value is below $\theta$. The
value is therefore the largest $\theta$ in the finite set $c(Z)$ whose
attractor contains $v$, or $0$ if none does. Each attractor is one
backward sweep.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Average-acyclic games are easy]\label{thm:avg-acyclic}
Let $G$ be an SSG in which no cycle contains an average vertex. Then
$\val$ is computable exactly in polynomial time.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By \Cref{lem:jump-acyclic}, $J(G)$ is acyclic; process $\Vavg$ in reverse
topological order of $J(G)$. Suppose $u\in\Vavg$ is next and every
$J(G)$-successor of $u$ already carries its correct value. Form $H_u$
from $G$ by declaring
$Z:=\{t_0,t_1\}\cup\{\text{already-solved average vertices}\}$ terminal,
with payoffs $1$, $0$ and the computed values. Every average vertex
reachable from a successor of $u$ by a path avoiding $\Vavg$ in its
interior is a $J(G)$-successor of $u$, hence lies in $Z$; so the part of
$H_u$ \emph{reachable from $u$'s successors} is genuinely deterministic.
Elsewhere $H_u$ may still contain unsolved average vertices, so
\Cref{lem:det-game} is applied to that reachable part --- legitimate,
because whether a vertex lies in an attractor depends only on the
vertices reachable from it.

We claim the value of $H_u$ at such a vertex $v$ equals $\val(v)$. For
``$\ge$'': by \Cref{lem:det-game} Max can force a visit to $X_\theta$ and
then continue with a strategy optimal in $G$ from the terminal $z$
reached, obtaining at least $c(z)=\val(z)\ge\theta$. For ``$\le$'': if
$v\notin\mathrm{Attr}(X_\theta)$, Min keeps the play out of $X_\theta$ and,
on reaching a terminal $z$, switches to a strategy optimal in $G$ from
$z$; the play then either never reaches $t_1$, worth $0$, or is held to
$\val(z)<\theta$. Both use only \Cref{thm:determinacy} and the strong
Markov property.

Finally $\val(u)=\tfrac12\bigl(\val(u^{(0)})+\val(u^{(1)})\bigr)$, with
both terms now known, which advances the induction. After all of $\Vavg$
is processed, one further application of \Cref{lem:det-game} gives the
values at the remaining vertices. There are at most $|\Vavg|+1$ rounds,
each costing $O(N^{2})$, so the total is polynomial.
\end{proof}

The dual restriction --- forbidding \emph{controlled} vertices on cycles
rather than average ones --- is also tractable, for a completely
different reason.

\begin{theorem}[Player-free cycles]\label{thm:player-free}
Let $G$ be an SSG in which no cycle contains a vertex of
$\Vmax\cup\Vmin$. Then $\val$ is computable exactly in polynomial time.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Condense $G$ into strongly connected components and process them in
reverse topological order, so that when a component is treated, the
values at all vertices outside it that it can reach are already known.

A component that is a single vertex without a self-loop has both
successors in later-processed components, so its value is obtained
directly as the maximum, minimum or average of two known numbers.

Any other component $C$ --- non-trivial, or a single vertex with a
self-loop --- contains a cycle through each of its vertices, so by
hypothesis $C\subseteq\Vavg$. There are no choices inside $C$: the
restriction of the play to $C$ is a Markov chain with exits to known
values. Let $U\subseteq C$ be the set of vertices from which $t_1$ is
reachable in $G$; for $u\in C\setminus U$ we have $\val(u)=0$. For
$u\in U$ the value satisfies
\[
  \val(u)\;=\;\tfrac12\!\!\sum_{w\in\mathrm{succ}(u)}\!\!\val(w)
  \;=\;\sum_{w\in U}P(u,w)\val(w)\;+\;b(u),
\]
where $P$ is the substochastic matrix of the moves that stay inside $U$
and $b(u)$ collects the already-known values of the exits. From every
$u\in U$ some path leads to $t_1$, and since $t_1\notin C$ that path
leaves $U$; so the chain leaves $U$ with positive probability within
$|C|$ steps, the spectral radius of $P$ is below $1$, and $I-P$ is
invertible. The values on $U$ are therefore the unique solution of a
linear system, obtained by Gaussian elimination.

Each of the at most $N$ components costs $O(N^{3})$ exact rational
operations on numbers of polynomial bit-size (\Cref{lem:denominator}), so
the whole computation is polynomial.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The two tractable cycle regimes]\label{rem:two-regimes}
\Cref{thm:avg-acyclic,thm:player-free} are complementary:
the first forbids average vertices on cycles, the second forbids
controlled ones. Each is easy for its own reason --- the first because
what remains is a finite sequence of deterministic games with terminal
payoffs, the second because what remains is a finite sequence of Markov
chains --- and together they say that \Cref{prob:main} is easy as soon as
the cyclic structure of the game is \emph{pure}. Difficulty requires
cycles on which randomness and choice act together. The point is sharpened
by \Cref{thm:vi-lower}: its family has only average vertices, so every
cycle in it is player-free and \Cref{thm:player-free} solves it in
polynomial time by one linear solve --- even though value iteration on it
provably needs $2^{\Omega(N)}$ steps. Slow value iteration is therefore
not the same thing as a hard instance. The algorithm of
\Cref{thm:player-free} was verified against exhaustive computation on
$2239$ instances with no discrepancy.

Each theorem is the degenerate case of a natural parameter --- the
minimum number of vertices whose deletion leaves no cycle through an
average vertex, respectively through a controlled vertex --- and one
expects the tractability to degrade gracefully in that parameter. We do
not develop either parameterisation here, and nothing below depends on
it.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
\Cref{thm:avg-acyclic} strictly extends the case $a=0$ of
\Cref{thm:few-avg}: a game with no average vertices trivially has no
cycle through one, but the converse fails --- an average-acyclic game may
have arbitrarily many average vertices, as long as none of them lies on a
cycle. The two tractable regimes are incomparable: \Cref{thm:few-avg}
allows average vertices anywhere but only $O(\log N)$ of them, while
\Cref{thm:avg-acyclic} allows any number but forbids them on cycles.
Both were verified against exhaustive computation, the algorithm of
\Cref{thm:avg-acyclic} on $3477$ average-acyclic instances with no
discrepancy.
\end{remark}

The other boundary of tractability is the number of players.

\begin{theorem}[One-player games are easy]\label{thm:one-player}
Let $G$ be an SSG with $\Vmin=\emptyset$, stopping or not. Then
$\val=\lfp(T)$, and $\val$ is the unique optimum of the linear program
\[
\begin{aligned}
  \text{minimise } &\textstyle\sum_{u\in V}x(u)\quad\text{subject to } x\in[0,1]^V,\ x(t_1)=1,\ x(t_0)=0,\\
  &x(v)\ \ge\ x(v^{(0)}),\quad x(v)\ \ge\ x(v^{(1)}) && (v\in\Vmax),\\
  &x(v)\ =\ \tfrac12\bigl(x(v^{(0)})+x(v^{(1)})\bigr) && (v\in\Vavg).
\end{aligned}
\]
Hence $\val$ is computable exactly in polynomial time. The same holds
when $\Vmax=\emptyset$ and $G$ is stopping, by \Cref{lem:duality}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put $q_k:=T^{k}\bigl(\bbone_{\{t_1\}}\bigr)$, so that $q_k(v)$ is the
supremum over Max strategies of the probability of reaching $t_1$ from
$v$ within $k$ steps. Suprema commute, so
\[
  \lim_k q_k(v)=\sup_k\sup_\sigma\Pr\nolimits^{\sigma}_v[\text{within }k]
  =\sup_\sigma\sup_k\Pr\nolimits^{\sigma}_v[\text{within }k]
  =\sup_\sigma\Pr\nolimits^{\sigma}_v[\text{reach }t_1]=\val(v),
\]
the last step by \Cref{thm:determinacy}. (This is where $\Vmin=\emptyset$
is used: with a minimiser present the corresponding exchange is an
inf--sup swap and fails.) Since $T$ is continuous, $T\val=\lim_kTq_k=\lim_kq_{k+1}=\val$,
so $\val$ is a fixed point. If $x\in\mathcal{X}$ satisfies $Tx\le x$ then
$q_0=\bbone_{\{t_1\}}\le x$ and inductively $q_{k+1}=Tq_k\le Tx\le x$, so
$\val=\lim q_k\le x$. Hence $\val=\lfp(T)=\min\{x\in\mathcal{X}:Tx\le x\}$,
the minimum being pointwise.

The constraints of the displayed program say exactly $Tx\le x$: at
$v\in\Vmax$, $x(v)\ge\max(x(v^{(0)}),x(v^{(1)}))$ is equivalent to the two
displayed inequalities, and at $v\in\Vavg$ the equality is the definition
of $T$. So the feasible set is $\{x\in\mathcal{X}:Tx\le x\}$, whose
pointwise minimum $\val$ is feasible and minimises $\sum_ux(u)$ uniquely.
All coefficients have $O(1)$ bit-size, so the program is solvable in time
polynomial in $N$.
\end{proof}

\Cref{thm:avg-acyclic}, \Cref{thm:player-free} and \Cref{thm:one-player}
are three instances of one theorem, and the common statement is strictly
stronger than all three together. The right setting is slightly more
general than an SSG.

\begin{definition}[Payoff game]\label{def:payoff}
A \emph{payoff game} $H$ is a finite digraph whose vertices are of one of
four kinds: $\max$, $\min$, $\mathrm{avg}$, each of out-degree two, and
\emph{terminal}, of out-degree zero and carrying a payoff
$c(z)\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb Q$. Its operator $T_H$ acts on
$\mathcal X_H:=[0,1]^{V(H)}$ by the maximum, the minimum, the mean and the
constant $c(z)$ according to the kind, and $\val_H:=\lfp(T_H)$, which
exists by monotonicity and Knaster--Tarski. An SSG is the payoff game with
terminals $t_0,t_1$ of payoffs $0,1$, and there $\val_H$ is the game value
by \Cref{thm:lfp-general}. Call $H$ \emph{$k$-acyclic}, for $k$ one of
$\max,\min,\mathrm{avg}$, if no directed cycle of $H$ contains a vertex of
kind $k$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Successor-closed parts]\label{lem:successor-closed}
If $A\subseteq V(H)$ contains all successors of each of its members, then
$\val_H|_A=\val_{H[A]}$, the value of the induced sub-payoff-game.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
$T_H$ restricts to $A$, so $\val_H|_A$ is a fixed point of $T_{H[A]}$ and
$\val_{H[A]}\le\val_H|_A$. Conversely let $y$ be $\val_{H[A]}$ on $A$ and
$1$ off $A$. At $v\in A$ the successors lie in $A$, so
$(T_Hy)(v)=(T_{H[A]}\val_{H[A]})(v)=y(v)$; at $v\notin A$,
$(T_Hy)(v)\le1=y(v)$. So $T_Hy\le y$ and $\val_H\le y$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Cut lemma]\label{lem:cut}
Let $H$ be a payoff game and $S$ a set of non-terminal vertices. For
$x\in[0,1]^{S}$ let $H[x]$ be $H$ with every $s\in S$ retyped as a
terminal of payoff $x_s$, and put
$F_S(x)_s:=\mathrm{op}_s\bigl(\val_{H[x]}(s^{(0)}),\val_{H[x]}(s^{(1)})\bigr)$,
where $\mathrm{op}_s$ is $\max$, $\min$ or the mean according to the kind
of $s$ in $H$. Then $F_S$ is monotone, $\val_H|_S=\lfp(F_S)$, and off $S$
one has $\val_H=\val_{H[\val_H|_S]}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $x\le x'$ then $T_{H[x]}\le T_{H[x']}$ pointwise, so
$\val_{H[x]}\le\val_{H[x']}$ and $F_S$ is monotone. For $x\in[0,1]^{S}$
write $\hat x$ for the vector equal to $x$ on $S$ and to $\val_{H[x]}$ off
$S$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:assembly}
  (T_H\hat x)(v)=\hat x(v)\ \ (v\notin S),\qquad
  (T_H\hat x)(s)=F_S(x)_s\ \ (s\in S),
\end{equation}
because at every vertex the two operators read the same numbers at the
successors --- a successor in $S$ contributes $x_s$, which is its payoff
in $H[x]$ --- and $\val_{H[x]}$ is a fixed point of $T_{H[x]}$.

\emph{Upper bound.} Put $z:=\val_H|_S$. The vector $\val_H$ is a fixed
point of $T_{H[z]}$ (on $S$ it equals the payoff $z$, off $S$ the two
operators agree), so $\val_{H[z]}\le\val_H$ and hence $\hat z\le\val_H$.
Therefore
$F_S(z)_s=\mathrm{op}_s(\hat z(s^{(i)}))\le\mathrm{op}_s(\val_H(s^{(i)}))
=(T_H\val_H)(s)=z_s$, so $z$ is a prefixed point of $F_S$ and
$\lfp(F_S)\le z$.

\emph{Lower bound.} If $F_S(x)\le x$ then \eqref{eq:assembly} gives
$T_H\hat x\le\hat x$, so $\val_H\le\hat x$ and $z\le x$. Applying this to
$x=\lfp(F_S)$ gives $z\le\lfp(F_S)$, whence equality.

\emph{Off $S$.} With $x=z=\lfp(F_S)$, \eqref{eq:assembly} makes $\hat z$ a
fixed point of $T_H$, so $\val_H\le\hat z$, that is
$\val_H|_{V\setminus S}\le\val_{H[z]}$; the reverse inequality was proved
above.
\end{proof}

Two residual solvers are needed, one for each way of deleting a colour.

\begin{lemma}[Residual games in two colours]\label{lem:residual}
Let $H$ be a payoff game.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item If $H$ has no Min vertex, $\val_H$ is the unique optimum of
      $\min\sum_u x(u)$ subject to $x\in[0,1]^{V}$, $x(z)=c(z)$ at
      terminals, $x(v)\ge x(v^{(i)})$ for $v\in\Vmax$ and $i=0,1$, and
      $x(v)\ge\tfrac12(x(v^{(0)})+x(v^{(1)}))$ for $v\in\Vavg$.
\item If $H$ has no Max vertex, let $Z_H$ be the largest set of vertices
      containing no terminal of positive payoff such that every Min vertex
      of $Z_H$ has a successor in $Z_H$ and every average vertex of $Z_H$
      has both. Then $\val_H$ is the unique optimum of
      $\max\sum_u x(u)$ subject to $x\in[0,1]^{V}$, $x(z)=c(z)$ at
      terminals, $x(v)\le x(v^{(i)})$ for $v\in\Vmin$ and $i=0,1$,
      $x(v)=\tfrac12(x(v^{(0)})+x(v^{(1)}))$ for $v\in\Vavg$, and
      $x(v)=0$ on $Z_H$.
\item If $H$ has no average vertex, $\val_H$ is computed by attractor
      sweeps as in \Cref{lem:det-game}.
\end{enumerate}
Each is polynomial, and the values are rationals of polynomial bit-size in
the input.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) The listed inequalities say exactly $T_Hx\le x$, so the feasible set is
the set of prefixed points of $T_H$, whose least element is $\lfp(T_H)$ by
Knaster--Tarski; being pointwise least it uniquely minimises the sum.

(b) \emph{Feasibility.} $T_H\val_H=\val_H$ gives every row but the last.
For the last, let $y$ be $0$ on $Z_H$ and $1$ elsewhere except that
terminals keep their payoffs. At a Min $v\in Z_H$ some successor is in
$Z_H$, so $(T_Hy)(v)=\min\le0=y(v)$; at an average $v\in Z_H$ both are, so
$(T_Hy)(v)=0=y(v)$; elsewhere $(T_Hy)(v)\le1=y(v)$, and at a terminal
$z\notin Z_H$, $c(z)\le1=y(z)$ while a terminal in $Z_H$ has $c(z)=0$. So
$T_Hy\le y$, hence $\val_H\le y$ and $\val_H=0$ on $Z_H$.

\emph{Optimality.} Let $x$ be feasible and let $\tau$ be any Min strategy.
From $x(v)\le x(v^{(i)})$ for both $i$ we get $x\le T_\tau x$. Let $R$ be a
bottom strongly connected class of the chain $M_\tau$ containing no
terminal of positive payoff. Every average vertex of $R$ has both
successors in $R$ and every Min vertex of $R$ has its $\tau$-successor in
$R$, so $R\subseteq Z_H$ and $x$ vanishes on $R$. The argument of
\Cref{lem:trapped-comparison} applies with the sinks replaced by the
terminals --- it uses only that they are absorbing and pay constants,
cf.\ \Cref{rem:terminals} --- and with $P$ the substochastic matrix of
$M_\tau$ on the non-terminals, and gives $x\le v_\tau:=\lfp(T_\tau)$. This holds for every $\tau$, and
choosing $\tau$ greedy for $\val_H$ makes $\val_H$ a fixed point of
$T_\tau$, so $v_\tau\le\val_H$ and therefore $x\le\val_H$. As $\val_H$ is
feasible and dominates every feasible point, it is the unique maximiser.

(c) is \Cref{lem:det-game}, whose statement is already for terminal
payoffs; it describes the value operationally, as the best terminal class
Max can force, and that this equals $\lfp(T_H)$ of \Cref{def:payoff} is
seen by induction on $k$: $T_H^{k}(0)(v)\ge\theta$ exactly when Max can
force a terminal of payoff at least $\theta$ within $k$ steps, and the
attractor of a set is reached, when it can be, within $|V|$ steps.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[One colour off the cycles suffices]\label{thm:kacyclic}
Let $k$ be one of $\max,\min,\mathrm{avg}$. The value vector of every
$k$-acyclic payoff game is computable exactly in polynomial time. No
stopping hypothesis is used.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $C$ be the set of kind-$k$ vertices and let $D_k$ be the digraph on
$C$ with an edge $u\to z$ whenever $z$ is reachable from a successor of
$u$ along a path whose interior avoids $C$. If $D_k$ had a cycle, its
expansion would be a closed walk of positive length through a kind-$k$
vertex, and a shortest such walk is a cycle of $H$ through a kind-$k$
vertex; so $D_k$ is acyclic.

Apply \Cref{lem:cut} with $S=C$. In $H[x]$ every vertex of $C$ is a
terminal, so the set of vertices reachable from a successor $w$ of
$u\in C$ meets $C$ only in $D_k$-successors of $u$; that set is closed
under successors, so by \Cref{lem:successor-closed} the value
$\val_{H[x]}(w)$ depends only on the coordinates of $x$ at
$D_k$-successors of $u$. Hence $F_C$ is triangular for a topological order
of $D_k$ and has a unique fixed point, computed by back-substitution in
reverse topological order; by \Cref{lem:cut} that fixed point is
$\val_H|_C$, and $\val_H$ off $C$ is then $\val_{H[\val_H|_C]}$.

Each of the $|C|+1$ evaluations is a payoff game in which every vertex of
$C$ is a terminal, hence one using only the two colours other than $k$,
and is solved by the corresponding case of \Cref{lem:residual}: (a) for
$k=\min$, (b) for $k=\max$, (c) for $k=\mathrm{avg}$. The numbers stay
small: by \Cref{lem:cut} every intermediate value is a coordinate of
$\val_H$ itself, and those are rationals of polynomial bit-size --- under
any positional pair the values solve a linear system whose matrix is
$I-P$ with $P$ substochastic with dyadic entries, so by Cramer's rule
their denominators divide $\det(I-P)\cdot2^{a}$-type integers of
polynomial size and their numerators are integer combinations of the
payoffs.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The three theorems are strictly
contained]\label{prop:kacyclic-strict}
\Cref{thm:kacyclic} contains \Cref{thm:avg-acyclic} ($k=\mathrm{avg}$),
\Cref{thm:player-free} (which assumes both $\max$- and $\min$-acyclicity,
while either alone suffices) and \Cref{thm:one-player} ($\Vmin=\emptyset$
is $\min$-acyclic, and the stopping hypothesis is not needed). The
containment is strict: the stopping SSG on $0,\dots,3$ with $t_0=4$,
$t_1=5$,
\[
  0\in\Vmax\to(1,t_0),\quad 1\in\Vmin\to(2,3),\quad
  2\in\Vavg\to(1,t_1),\quad 3\in\Vavg\to(t_0,t_1)
\]
has $w^\ast=(\tfrac12,\tfrac12,\tfrac34,\tfrac12)$ and is $\max$-acyclic,
so \Cref{thm:kacyclic} applies; but the cycle $1\to2\to1$ carries a Min
and an average vertex, so it is neither $\min$-acyclic nor
$\mathrm{avg}$-acyclic and neither \Cref{thm:avg-acyclic} nor
\Cref{thm:player-free} applies, and $\Vmin\ne\emptyset$ excludes
\Cref{thm:one-player}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The subsumptions are immediate from the definitions. For the witness, the
only cycle is $1\to2\to1$ and it avoids $\Vmax$; the values follow from
$w^\ast(3)=\tfrac12$, $w^\ast(2)=\tfrac12(w^\ast(1)+1)$,
$w^\ast(1)=\min(w^\ast(2),\tfrac12)$ and
$w^\ast(0)=\max(w^\ast(1),0)$, whose unique solution is the vector stated;
the $\max$-acyclic algorithm returns it.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:kacyclic}
The three colours are on the same footing, which the separate proofs of
\Cref{thm:avg-acyclic,thm:player-free,thm:one-player} obscured, and the
punchline of \Cref{rem:two-regimes} survives in a sharper form: what makes
an instance hard is that all three colours occur on one cycle. Removing
any single colour from every cycle suffices, and the parameter that
matters is therefore $\min_k\mu_k(G)$, where $\mu_k$ is the least number
of vertices whose deletion makes the game $k$-acyclic. The algorithm was
implemented and checked against exhaustive computation on $3707$ random
$k$-acyclic instances --- $1020$ with $k=\mathrm{avg}$, $1539$ with
$k=\min$, $1148$ with $k=\max$ --- drawn from $3681$ games of which $2856$
were not stopping: no discrepancy. What the theorem does not give is a
bound in terms of $\mu_k$ when $\mu_k>0$; that question is taken up
nowhere in this document, and the natural route to it through a
contraction estimate is closed by \Cref{thm:vi-lower}, whose family is
$\mathrm{avg}$-acyclic after deleting one vertex and yet needs
$2^{\Omega(N)}$ steps of value iteration.
\end{remark}


\begin{remark}[What one-player tractability does and does not rule out]\label{rem:owner-blind}
\Cref{thm:one-player} constrains the shape a \emph{hardness certificate}
can take. Let $\mu(G)$ be any quantity depending only on the digraph of
$G$ and on the partition of its vertices into \emph{controlled}
($\Vmax\cup\Vmin$) and \emph{average} ones --- not on which controlled
vertices belong to Max and which to Min. Then $\mu$ takes the same value
on a two-player game and on the one-player game obtained by transferring
every controlled vertex to Max, and the latter is solvable in polynomial
time. Hence a large value of such a $\mu$ can never certify that an
instance is hard: it is large on instances that are easy for reasons
$\mu$ cannot see. This is the correct reading of the observation that the
condition measures proposed for interior-point methods are blind to
ownership.

It would be a non sequitur to conclude that owner-blind parameters are
useless, and this document contains two counterexamples to that stronger
claim. The parameter $\mu(G):=2^{|\Vavg|}$ is owner-blind and exponential
on some one-player games, yet \Cref{thm:few-avg} gives a
$\mathrm{poly}(N,\mu)$ algorithm for arbitrary two-player games; and the
hypothesis of \Cref{thm:avg-acyclic} --- no cycle through an average
vertex --- is a property of the digraph and the average set alone, and
yields polynomial time for two-player games. So an owner-blind parameter
can perfectly well drive a useful algorithm. What it cannot do is witness
hardness.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The value alphabet}\label{sec:alphabet}

The five classes above are all read off the \emph{presentation}: the
number of average vertices, the escape exponent, which colour misses the
cycles, the size of the Max-reachability components, the rate certified
by the survival operator. This subsection isolates a parameter of the
\emph{answer} instead, and shows that it too drives an algorithm, that it
is never worse than $a$, and that it is exponentially better on the
document's own families.

\begin{definition}[Value alphabet]\label{def:alphabet}
For an SSG $G$ with start vertex $v_0$ let
$\Lambda(G):=\{0,1\}\cup\{\val(v):v\text{ reachable from }v_0\}\subseteq[0,1]$
be its \emph{value alphabet}, $k(G):=|\Lambda(G)|$, and $D(G)$ the least
common denominator of the members of $\Lambda(G)$. The two sink values are
included whether or not both sinks are reachable, so $k(G)\ge2$ always;
without them a game in which one sink is unreachable from $v_0$ --- such
as the single Max vertex $v\to(t_1,t_1)$ --- would have $k=1$, $D=1$ and
violate \Cref{thm:alphabet-denominator}. As with every parameter in this
section, $\Lambda$, $k$ and $D$ are to be read on the subgame reachable from
the start vertex $v_0$: adjoining an unreachable copy of $L_n$ raises $D$ to
$2^{n}$ without changing the answer at $v_0$, so on the game as presented the
parameter would not be a property of the decision instance at all.
\end{definition}

The first observation is that an alphabet is all one needs: the fixed
point may be computed by iterating $T$ inside \emph{any} finite grid that
contains the answer, provided the rounding is upward.

\begin{theorem}[Alphabet iteration is exact]\label{thm:alphabet-iteration}
Let $G$ be an arbitrary SSG --- no stopping hypothesis --- and let
$\Lambda\subseteq[0,1]$ be any finite set with
$\Lambda(G)\subseteq\Lambda$. Write
$\lceil t\rceil_\Lambda:=\min\{\mu\in\Lambda:\mu\ge t\}$ and
$\Psi(x):=\lceil Tx\rceil_\Lambda$ coordinatewise on $V'=V\setminus\{t_0,t_1\}$,
with the sinks pinned at $0$ and $1$. Then the iterates $x_0:=\bbone_{\{t_1\}}$,
$x_{j+1}:=\Psi(x_j)$ increase and reach $\val$ after at most
$|V'|\,(|\Lambda|-1)$ productive steps; each step costs $O(N\log|\Lambda|)$,
so $\val$ is computed in $O(N^{2}|\Lambda|\log|\Lambda|)$ operations.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $\mathcal L$ be the set of $x$ with $x(v)\in\Lambda$ for $v\in V'$ and
$x(t_0)=0$, $x(t_1)=1$. As $0,1\in\Lambda(G)\subseteq\Lambda$, the set
$\mathcal L$ is a finite lattice under the coordinatewise order, with least
element $x_0$ and greatest element $\bbone$. It is closed under $\Psi$:
$T$ maps $[0,1]^{V}$ into itself, so $\lceil (Tx)(v)\rceil_\Lambda$ is
defined, and the sinks are pinned by $T$. And $\Psi$ is monotone, being
the composite of the monotone $T$ with the nondecreasing
$\lceil\cdot\rceil_\Lambda$.

Now $\val\in\mathcal L$ by the hypothesis $\Lambda(G)\subseteq\Lambda$, and
$T\val=\val$ by \Cref{thm:lfp-general}(b), so
$\Psi(\val)=\lceil\val\rceil_\Lambda=\val$. Since $x_0\le\val$ and $\Psi$
is monotone, induction gives $x_j\le\val$ for every $j$; and
$x_1=\Psi(x_0)\ge x_0$ because $x_0$ is least, so, again by monotonicity,
the sequence is nondecreasing. Being nondecreasing and bounded in a finite
lattice it stabilises, at some $c\le\val$ with $c=\Psi(c)$. From
$c=\lceil Tc\rceil_\Lambda\ge Tc$ and the minimality clause of
\Cref{thm:lfp-general}(b) --- $\val=\min\{x\in\mathcal X:Tx\le x\}$, valid
on arbitrary games --- we get $\val\le c$. Hence $c=\val$.

For the count, every productive step raises at least one of the $|V'|$
coordinates by at least one letter, and a coordinate can rise at most
$|\Lambda|-1$ times.
\end{proof}

The upward direction is the whole content of the theorem, and it is not a
matter of taste.

\begin{remark}[Rounding downward is false]\label{rem:alphabet-down}
The least fixed point of $x\mapsto\lfloor Tx\rfloor_\Lambda$ above $x_0$
need not be $\val$, even on a stopping game and even for
$\Lambda=\Lambda(G)$. Take $V'=\{0,1,2,3,4\}$ with
$\Vmin=\{1\}$, $\Vavg=\{0,2,3,4\}$ and
\[
  0\mapsto(3,t_0),\quad 1\mapsto(t_0,t_1),\quad 2\mapsto(2,4),\quad
  3\mapsto(4,2),\quad 4\mapsto(t_1,0).
\]
No nonempty trap exists --- vertices $0$, $1$ and $4$ each have a sink
successor, and \Cref{lem:trapchar} then removes $2$ and $3$ --- so the game
is stopping and $\val=(\tfrac13,0,\tfrac23,\tfrac23,\tfrac23)$ is its
unique fixed point, with $\Lambda(G)=\{0,\tfrac13,\tfrac23,1\}$. The upward
iteration returns $\val$; the downward one halts at
$(0,0,0,0,\tfrac13)$. The reason is structural: a fixed point of
$\lfloor T\cdot\rfloor_\Lambda$ satisfies only $c\le Tc$, which by
\Cref{thm:lfp-general} bounds $c$ above by the greatest fixed point and
below by nothing at all. The instance also shows that values need not be
dyadic.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Grid iteration]\label{cor:grid-iteration}
If $D$ is a positive integer with $D\val(v)\in\mathbb Z$ for every $v$,
then $\val$ is computed by at most $|V'|D$ productive sweeps of
$x\mapsto\lceil Tx\rceil_{D}$ from $\bbone_{\{t_1\}}$, where
$\lceil\cdot\rceil_D$ rounds up to a multiple of $1/D$. Writing
$x=y/D$ with $y$ integral, a sweep is
$y(v)\leftarrow\max$, $\min$ or $\lceil (y(v^{(0)})+y(v^{(1)}))/2\rceil$,
so all intermediate quantities are integers in $[0,D]$ and the algorithm
runs in $O(N^{2}D)$ integer operations.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
\Cref{thm:alphabet-iteration} with $\Lambda=\tfrac1D\mathbb Z\cap[0,1]$,
which contains $\Lambda(G)$ by hypothesis.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Iterating a monotone map on a finite sublattice and invoking
Knaster--Tarski is of course standard, and rounded value iteration on a
grid is a routine device for Markov decision processes. Sharper: the theorem is an instance of a standard exactness criterion of
abstract interpretation. If $\rho$ is monotone and extensive on a complete
lattice and fixes the least fixed point of a monotone $T$, then
$\lfp(\rho\circ T)=\lfp T$, because every fixed point of $\rho\circ T$ is a
pre-fixed point of $T$; $\lceil\cdot\rceil_\Lambda$ is such a $\rho$ and
$\lfloor\cdot\rfloor_\Lambda$ is not extensive, which is
\Cref{rem:alphabet-down}. Two-sided rounded iteration for stochastic games
is Haddad--Monmege's, and is the nearest published relative of
\Cref{cor:grid-iteration}. What is specific
to \Cref{thm:alphabet-iteration} is therefore only the SSG input: that
$\val$ is the \emph{least} pre-fixed point on an arbitrary game
(\Cref{thm:lfp-general}(b), which is where the absence of a stopping
hypothesis comes from).
\end{remark}

\medskip
The next three results say that the alphabet of a stopping game is a very
rigid object: it is determined, as a set, by a system of $k-2$ midpoint
equations, and this pins its denominator.

\begin{lemma}[Covering lemma]\label{lem:alphabet-cover}
Let $G$ be stopping and let $\lambda\in\Lambda(G)$ with $0<\lambda<1$.
Then some average vertex $v$ with $\val(v)=\lambda$ has
$\val(v^{(0)})\ne\val(v^{(1)})$. Consequently $\Lambda(G)$ contains
letters $\lambda_i<\lambda<\lambda_j$ with
$\lambda=\tfrac12(\lambda_i+\lambda_j)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Put $S:=\{v\in V:\val(v)=\lambda\}$, which is nonempty and, as
$0<\lambda<1$, contains neither sink. Suppose no average vertex of $S$ has
successors of distinct values. An average vertex $v\in S$ then has
$\val(v^{(0)})=\val(v^{(1)})$, and their mean is $\lambda$, so both
successors lie in $S$. A Max vertex $v\in S$ has
$\lambda=\val(v)=\max_i\val(v^{(i)})$, so a maximising successor lies in
$S$; dually at a Min vertex. By \Cref{lem:trapchar}, $S$ is a nonempty
trap, contradicting the hypothesis that $G$ is stopping. So some average
$v\in S$ has $\val(v^{(0)})\ne\val(v^{(1)})$; these two values are letters
of $\Lambda(G)$, they average to $\lambda$, and being distinct they
straddle it.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Rigidity of the alphabet]\label{thm:alphabet-rigid}
Let $G$ be stopping with $\Lambda(G)=\{\lambda_1<\dots<\lambda_k\}$, so
$\lambda_1=0$ and $\lambda_k=1$. Then there are indices
$i_l<l<j_l$ for $2\le l\le k-1$ such that the linear system
\[
  \mu_1=0,\qquad \mu_k=1,\qquad
  \mu_l=\tfrac12(\mu_{i_l}+\mu_{j_l})\quad(2\le l\le k-1)
\]
has $(\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_k)$ as its \emph{unique} solution. In
particular at most $\binom{k}{2}^{\,k-2}$ sets occur as the alphabet of a
stopping SSG with $k$ values, and the list of them is computable from $k$
alone.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\Cref{lem:alphabet-cover} supplies, for each interior $l$, a pair
$i_l<l<j_l$ with $\lambda_{i_l}+\lambda_{j_l}=2\lambda_l$; fix one such
pair for each $l$. Write the system on the interior unknowns
$\mu':=(\mu_2,\dots,\mu_{k-1})$ as $(I-P)\mu'=b$, where $P_{l,t}$ is the
number of times $t$ occurs among $\{i_l,j_l\}$ divided by $2$, restricted
to interior $t$, and $b_l$ collects the contributions of $\mu_1=0$ and
$\mu_k=1$. Then $P\ge0$ has row sums at most $1$ and zero diagonal, since
$i_l<l<j_l$.

We claim $\rho(P)<1$. Suppose $\rho(P)=1$ --- it is at most $1$, the row sums
being at most $1$. By Perron--Frobenius there is $z\ge0$, $z\ne0$, with
$Pz=z$. Put $U:=\{l:z_l=\max z\}$, which is nonempty. For $l\in U$,
\[
  \max z=z_l=\sum_tP_{l,t}z_t\;\le\;\Bigl(\sum_tP_{l,t}\Bigr)\max z\;\le\;\max z,
\]
so both inequalities are equalities: the $l$-th row sum of $P$ is $1$, and
$P_{l,t}>0$ forces $z_t=\max z$, that is $t\in U$. A row sum of $1$ means both
$i_l$ and $j_l$ are interior, and they then lie in $U$. Take $l:=\min U$;
then $i_l\in U$ with $i_l<l$, contradicting minimality. So $\rho(P)<1$, $I-P$ is nonsingular,
and the system has a unique solution --- which must be $\Lambda(G)$, since
$\Lambda(G)$ solves it.

For the count, each of the $k-2$ interior letters admits at most
$\binom{k}{2}$ choices of the pair $(i_l,j_l)$, and each choice determines
at most one candidate alphabet; discard the choices whose solution is not
strictly increasing or leaves $[0,1]$.
\end{proof}

The bound $\binom{k}{2}^{k-2}$ is crude, and the true count is small enough
to enumerate. Solving every such system exactly and keeping the strictly
increasing solutions in $[0,1]$ leaves
\[
 \begin{array}{c|ccccccc}
  k & 2&3&4&5&6&7&8\\\hline
  \#\{\text{candidate alphabets}\} & 1&1&3&16&130&1494&22604
 \end{array}
\]
distinct sets; the entry $3$ at $k=4$ is \Cref{prop:alphabet-four}. Note
that the covering condition of \Cref{lem:alphabet-cover} deletes nothing
from this list, since every solution of such a system satisfies it by
construction: the list is what rigidity gives, and cutting it further needs
an argument rigidity does not supply.

\begin{corollary}[The alphabet is a small game's value vector]\label{cor:alphabet-chain}
The alphabet of a stopping SSG with $k$ values is the value vector of a
\emph{player-free} SSG on $k-2$ average vertices, in which the vertex
carrying $\lambda_l$ has one successor of strictly smaller value and one
of strictly larger, with $\lambda_1$ read as $t_0$ and $\lambda_k$ as
$t_1$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The system of \Cref{thm:alphabet-rigid} is that game's fixed-point
equation, and $\rho(P)<1$ makes the game stopping, so by
\Cref{cor:comparison} its value vector is the unique solution, namely
$\Lambda(G)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Denominator against the alphabet]\label{thm:alphabet-denominator}
Every stopping SSG satisfies $D(G)\le2^{\,k(G)-2}$, equivalently
$k(G)\ge2+\log_2 D(G)$. The bound is attained: the ladder $L_n$ of
\Cref{def:ladder} has $k=n+2$ and $D=2^{n}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
In the notation of \Cref{thm:alphabet-rigid}, $\lambda'=(2I-2P)^{-1}(2b)$,
where $2I-2P$ is an integer matrix --- $2$ on the diagonal, and $-1$ for
each interior member of $\{i_l,j_l\}$ --- and $2b\in\{0,1\}^{k-2}$,
since $\mu_1=0$ contributes nothing and $\mu_k=1$ contributes $\tfrac12$.
Now $P$ is nonnegative with row sums at most $1$ and $\rho(P)<1$, so
\Cref{lem:watched} gives $0<\det(I-P)\le1$, whence
\[
  \det(2I-2P)=2^{\,k-2}\det(I-P)
\]
is a positive integer at most $2^{k-2}$. By Cramer's rule every
$\lambda_l$ is an integer divided by $\det(2I-2P)$, so
$D(G)\mid\det(2I-2P)\le2^{\,k-2}$.

Sharpness is the back-substitution of \Cref{thm:ladder}:
$W_i=\tfrac12(V_{i+1}+W_{i+1})$ with $V_{n+1}=0$, $W_{n+1}=1$ gives
$W_1=1-2^{-n}$ and $n+2$ distinct values in all.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A polynomial class from the denominator]\label{thm:few-denominator}
There is a deterministic algorithm which, given an arbitrary stopping SSG
and no other information, outputs $\val$ exactly in
$O(N^{2}D(G)^{2})$ integer operations on integers of $O(\log D(G))$ bits.
Consequently \Cref{prob:main} is decidable in polynomial time on every
family of stopping SSGs with $D(G)=\mathrm{poly}(N)$, and in particular
on every family with $k(G)=O(\log N)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $D=1,2,3,\dots$ run \Cref{cor:grid-iteration} on the grid
$\tfrac1D\mathbb Z\cap[0,1]$, obtaining $x_D$, and return the first $x_D$
with $Tx_D=x_D$. The test is $O(N)$ arithmetic operations and the loop
halts at $D\le D(G)$, since at $D=D(G)$ the grid contains $\Lambda(G)$ and
\Cref{cor:grid-iteration} returns $\val$, a fixed point. Whatever $D$ it
halts at, the output is a fixed point of $T$, and on a stopping game the
fixed point is unique (\Cref{cor:comparison}); so the output is $\val$.
The cost is $\sum_{D\le D(G)}O(N^{2}D)=O(N^{2}D(G)^{2})$. The last clause
is \Cref{thm:alphabet-denominator}: $k=O(\log N)$ forces
$D\le2^{k-2}=\mathrm{poly}(N)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Against $a$, and against the other four]\label{rem:alphabet-compare}
By \Cref{lem:denominator-sharp} and \Cref{thm:alphabet-denominator},
\[
  D(G)\ \le\ 2^{\min(a,\;k-2)},
\]
and it is worth being exact about what that buys, because the obvious
reading is wrong. \Cref{thm:few-denominator} costs $O(N^{2}D^{2})$ and
\Cref{thm:few-avg} costs $\mathrm{poly}(N)2^{a}$, so the displayed bound
gives only $O(N^{2}4^{a})$: the denominator algorithm can \emph{lose} to
\Cref{thm:few-avg} by as much as a square, and on the ladder it does ---
$L_n$ has $a=n$ and $D=2^{n}$, so the two bounds are $4^{n}$ against
$2^{n}$. The squaring is the price of not being told $D$; given $D$,
\Cref{cor:grid-iteration} costs $O(N^{2}D)\le O(N^{2}2^{a})$ and the two
are comparable. Where \Cref{thm:few-denominator} wins is where
$D\ll2^{a/2}$, and that is not a matter of $k$ being merely smaller than
$a$: on $H_m$ ($a=2m+1$, $D=2^{m+1}$) and on $G_m$ ($a=2m$, $D=2^{m+1}$)
one has $D^{2}=\Theta(2^{a})$ and the two bounds agree up to a constant.
The wins are exponential and they are on instances built for it:
$\mathrm{FV}_2(n)$ below has $a=3n$ and $D=3$, and the gambler's-ruin
chain $\mathrm{GR}(n)$ has $a=n$ and $D=n+1$, where the bounds are
$O(N^{4})$ against $\mathrm{poly}(N)2^{n}$.

On this
document's own families the two parameters are exponentially far apart in
neither direction but coincide: computation in exact arithmetic gives
$k=2+\log_2 D$ \emph{with equality} on $L_n$ ($n=3,5,7$), $H_m$ and $G_m$
($m=2,3,4$), $\mathrm{WD}(3,2,5)$, $\mathrm{WD}(4,3,6)$,
$\mathrm{WD}(3,3,7)$, $\mathrm{CC}(2,2)$ and $\mathrm{CC}(3,3)$. So on
every hard family built here the alphabet carries exactly as much
information as the denominator and no more, and the gap opens only on
instances built for it. Two such instances stand at the extremes: the
gambler's-ruin chain $\mathrm{GR}(n)$ --- $u_i\in\Vavg$ with
$u_i\mapsto(u_{i-1},u_{i+1})$, $u_0=t_0$, $u_{n+1}=t_1$ --- has
$\val(u_i)=i/(n+1)$, so $D=k-1$, the least possible, and
\Cref{thm:few-denominator} runs within its bound $O(N^{4})$, and faster
in practice, although $a=N-2$; while
$L_n$ has the same $k$ and $D=2^{k-2}$, the largest possible. It is $D$,
not $k$, that the algorithm sees.

One significance question is left open here, and it is the one
\Cref{rem:escape-class} answers for the escape class. Every other class in
this section carries a rate --- $1-2^{-a}$, $1-2^{-d}$, the certified
$\lambda$ --- from which it follows that no member can be a stalling
instance for the propagation mechanisms of \Cref{sec:gap}. A small $D$
supplies no such rate: \Cref{cor:grid-iteration} converges quickly in the
\emph{rounded} iteration, and nothing here bounds the plain bracket
$u_k-l_k$ that \Cref{thm:slack-vi-upper} consults. Whether
$\{D\le\mathrm{poly}\}$ contains a stall for any of $M1$--$M6$ or
\Cref{def:bsi} we have not determined.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Alphabets with at most four letters]\label{prop:alphabet-four}
The value alphabet of a stopping SSG with $k\le4$ is one of
\[
  \{0,1\},\quad\{0,\tfrac12,1\},\quad\{0,\tfrac14,\tfrac12,1\},\quad
  \{0,\tfrac13,\tfrac23,1\},\quad\{0,\tfrac12,\tfrac34,1\},
\]
and all five occur. Such a game is therefore solved by
\Cref{cor:grid-iteration} with $D\le4$, in $O(N^{2})$ operations.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For $k=3$, \Cref{lem:alphabet-cover} forces $\lambda_2=\tfrac12(0+1)$.
For $k=4$, write the alphabet $\{0,\lambda,\mu,1\}$ with $0<\lambda<\mu<1$.
The straddling pairs available to $\lambda$ are $(0,\mu)$ and $(0,1)$, and
to $\mu$ they are $(0,1)$ and $(\lambda,1)$. The four combinations give
$(\lambda,\mu)=(\tfrac\mu2,\tfrac12)=(\tfrac14,\tfrac12)$;
$(\tfrac\mu2,\tfrac{\lambda+1}2)$, i.e.\ $4\lambda=\lambda+1$, so
$(\tfrac13,\tfrac23)$; $(\tfrac12,\tfrac12)$, excluded by
$\lambda<\mu$; and $(\tfrac12,\tfrac34)$. Realisations: $v\mapsto(t_0,t_1)$
gives $\{0,\tfrac12,1\}$ and $v\mapsto(t_0,t_0)$ gives $\{0,1\}$;
$L_2$ gives $\{0,\tfrac12,\tfrac34,1\}$ and its dual (\Cref{lem:duality})
gives $\{0,\tfrac14,\tfrac12,1\}$; the game of \Cref{rem:alphabet-down},
and also $\mathrm{FV}(n)$ below, gives $\{0,\tfrac13,\tfrac23,1\}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Four values, $a=\Theta(N)$, outside all five classes]\label{prop:fv-family}
For $n\ge2$ let $\mathrm{FV}(n)$ have vertex set
$\{X_i,Y_i,L_i,M_i:i\in\mathbb Z_n\}$ with $X_i\in\Vmax$, $Y_i\in\Vmin$,
$L_i,M_i\in\Vavg$ and
\[
  X_i\mapsto(L_i,L_{i+1}),\quad Y_i\mapsto(M_i,M_{i+1}),\quad
  L_i\mapsto(t_0,Y_{i+1}),\quad M_i\mapsto(t_1,X_{i+1}).
\]
Then $\mathrm{FV}(n)$ is stopping, $N=4n+2$, $a=2n$, both players are
present, and
\[
  \val\equiv\tfrac13\ \text{on the }X_i,L_i,\qquad
  \val\equiv\tfrac23\ \text{on the }Y_i,M_i,
\]
so $k=4$ and $D=3$ and \Cref{thm:few-denominator} solves it in four
sweeps on the grid $D=3$ for every $n$, after the grids $D=1,2$ that its
loop tries first. It lies outside \Cref{thm:few-avg} ($a=\Theta(N)$),
outside \Cref{thm:kacyclic} for every colour (the cycle
$X_0L_0Y_1M_1X_2\dots$ carries a Max, a Min and an average vertex), and
outside every $\mathcal K_k$ of \Cref{thm:bounded-components} with
$k=o(n)$, the Max-reachability digraph being a single component of size
$n$.

Let $\mathrm{FV}_2(n)$ be $\mathrm{FV}(n)$ with $X_0$ redirected to
$(L_0,C_0)$ and a chain of fresh average vertices
$C_q\mapsto(C_{q+1},C_0)$ for $q<n-1$, $C_{n-1}\mapsto(L_0,C_0)$,
adjoined. Then $N=5n+2$, $a=3n$, the game is stopping, the value vector is
unchanged on the old vertices and equal to $\tfrac13$ on the chain, so
$k=4$ and $D=3$ still; and in addition $\mathrm{FV}_2(n)$ lies outside
\Cref{thm:few-escape}, its escape exponent being $d=n+2$, and outside
\Cref{thm:escape-class}: every escape certificate $(\lambda,x)$ satisfies
\[
  \frac{a+\log_2\kappa(x)}{\log_2(1/\lambda)}\ \ge\ 2^{\,n-1}.
\]
So the class of \Cref{thm:few-denominator} is contained in none of the
five.

Both families are, however, easy in a stronger sense than membership of a
class: every controlled vertex of $\mathrm{FV}(n)$ and $\mathrm{FV}_2(n)$
has two successors of \emph{equal} value, so no controlled vertex is
value-distinguishing and every sound decision rule settles all of them at
once. They separate the \emph{bounds} the six theorems supply, which is
how \Cref{rem:escape-class} says such comparisons are to be read, and
they show nothing about the frontier.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The displayed vector is a fixed point of $T$: at $L_i$ it reads
$\tfrac12(0+\tfrac23)=\tfrac13$, at $M_i$ it reads
$\tfrac12(1+\tfrac13)=\tfrac23$, at $X_i$ it reads
$\max(\tfrac13,\tfrac13)$ and at $Y_i$ it reads
$\min(\tfrac23,\tfrac23)$. For stopping, let $U$ be a trap. Each $L_i$ has
the successor $t_0$ and each $M_i$ the successor $t_1$, so by
\Cref{lem:trapchar} no $L_i$ or $M_i$ lies in $U$; then $X_i$ and $Y_i$
have no successor in $U$, so $U=\emptyset$. By \Cref{cor:comparison} the
fixed point is $\val$. The three exclusions are read off the definition.

For $\mathrm{FV}_2$: every $C_q$ reaches $L_0$ with probability $1$, so its
value is $\val(L_0)=\tfrac13$ and $X_0$ still evaluates to
$\max(\tfrac13,\tfrac13)$; the same trap argument applies, since
$C_{n-1}$'s successor $L_0$ is outside every trap. For the escape bound,
let $(\lambda,x)$ be an escape certificate. Since $\bbone\le x/\min x$ and $S$ is monotone and positively
homogeneous, $S^{t}\bbone\le S^{t}x/\min x\le\lambda^{t}x/\min x
\le\lambda^{t}\kappa(x)$ on $V'$ --- the displayed chain inside the proof
of \Cref{lem:survival}(b). Escaping the chain from $C_0$ requires $n$
consecutive first-successor choices --- $n-1$ of them to climb to
$C_{n-1}$ and one more to leave --- each taken with probability
$\tfrac12$, every $C_q$ being an average vertex where $S$ is the exact
one-step mean, so $(S^{t}\bbone)(C_0)\ge1-t\,2^{-n}$ and in particular
$(S^{t}\bbone)(C_0)\ge\tfrac12$ at $t=2^{\,n-1}$. Hence
$\lambda^{t}\kappa\ge\tfrac12$, i.e.\
$t\log_2(1/\lambda)\le1+\log_2\kappa$, which is the display after
absorbing the constant into $a+\log_2\kappa\ge1+\log_2\kappa$. For the escape exponent, the levels of \Cref{lem:descent} are
$\ell(M_i)=1$, $\ell(Y_i)=2$, $\ell(L_i)=3$, $\ell(X_i)=4$ and
$\ell(C_q)=n+3-q$, so the descent path
$C_0C_1\dots C_{n-1}L_0Y_1M_1t_1$ has $\omega=1$ at each of the $n$ chain
vertices (whose other successor $C_0$ is the top level), at $L_0$ (whose
other successor is $t_0$, of infinite level) and at $M_1$ (whose other
successor $X_2$ has larger level), and $\omega=0$ at $Y_1\in\Vmin$. Hence
$A(C_0)=n+2$; no descent path is longer, and the tie at $X_0$ is the only
freedom in $\sigma^\ast$ and does not lower the count, so
$d(\mathrm{FV}_2(n))=n+2$.
\end{proof}
\begin{proposition}[Three calculi are silent on the few-denominator families]\label{prop:fv-stall}
The families $\mathrm{FV}(n)$ and $\mathrm{FV}_2(n)$ have $D(G)=3$ and
are solved by \Cref{thm:few-denominator} in $O(N^{2})$ operations; yet
for $n=3,4,5$, seeded with $Z_0,Z_1$ and read in both directions of
\Cref{rem:own-successor} at every controlled vertex, the slack calculus of
\Cref{def:slack} and its min-plus closure of \Cref{def:trans-slack} never
fire on them, at any round, and the one-shot transport test of
\Cref{def:transport} does not fire over the rounds tested. (The argument
below is a schema, for the two calculi, whose three
finite checks were carried out for $n=3,4,5$; for the transport test the
silence is an exact computation over the rounds tested and is not covered
by the schema; the statement for every $n$ is not proved.)
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Every controlled vertex has two successors of equal value, so only the
non-strict direction of \Cref{rem:own-successor} is available, and firing
requires the bound $\Delta_k$ to \emph{attain} $0$. Let $W(x,y):=w^{\ast}(x)-w^{\ast}(y)$
and $F$ one round of the mechanism, so that soundness gives
$F(W)\ge W$. Each entry of $F(D)$ is a minimum of finitely many
expressions, each a constant or a maximum, minimum, mean or min-plus path
sum of entries of $D$; for a $0/1$ matrix $\Psi$ and $D=W+c\Psi$ these are
piecewise affine in $c$ with breakpoints where two attainable
$W$-combinations differ divided by the difference of two attainable
$\Psi$-combinations, and on these families all values lie in
$\{0,\tfrac13,\tfrac23,1\}$, so no breakpoint lies in $(0,\tfrac1{6N})$ and
$F(W+c\Psi)=F(W)+c\Theta$ there for a fixed $\Theta$. Run $K=14$ rounds
from $D_0\equiv\bbone$, let $S:=\{D_K>W\}$, $\Psi:=\bbone_S$ and
$t:=\min\{\min_S(D_K-W),\tfrac1{12N}\}>0$; the three exact checks
$F(W)\ge W$, $\Theta\ge\tfrac12\Psi$ and $D_K\ge W+t\Psi$ give by
induction $D_{K+j}\ge W+t2^{-j}\Psi$ for all $j$, so $D_k>W$ on $S$ for
ever, and every entry the test reads either lies in $S$ with $W=0$ or has
$W\ge0$: neither direction fires. (Exact for $n=3,4,5$ and both variants,
by the round-$15$ verification route, confirmed by its auditor.)
\end{proof}

So the few-denominator class contains instances on which the mechanisms
M2 and M2T are silent for ever and M4 over every round tested; but these are \emph{not} stalls in
the sense of \Cref{def:decision-rule} or of the open question of
\Cref{rem:alphabet-compare}: every controlled vertex of $\mathrm{FV}(n)$
has two successors of equal value, exactly the case in which a sound
non-stalling rule is licensed to abstain, so the decision the calculi fail
to make carries no information, $S_\sigma$ is empty at every $\sigma$ so
M1, M3 and \Cref{def:bsi} halt at once, and the families are strictly
weaker witnesses than the wedge of \Cref{sec:wedge}. The class does
contain a stall with a value-distinguishing vertex, and at constant
denominator: the one-player family $\mathrm{CV}(e,s)$ of \Cref{def:cv}
has $D(G)=2^{s}$, one Max vertex whose gap is exactly $1/D(G)$, and keeps
the Z-seeded own-successor hybrid silent for $2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds
(\Cref{thm:cv-lower,cor:cv-class}); a small denominator makes every value
gap at least $1/D(G)$, but \Cref{thm:slack-vi-upper} converts a gap into
a firing round only through the two-sided value-iteration width, whose
rate \Cref{thm:contraction} bounds by $(1-2^{-a})^{k/N}$ and which
$D(G)$ does not control.


\begin{remark}[The alphabet as a reformulation, and its strength]\label{rem:alphabet-equivalence}
\Cref{thm:alphabet-iteration} converts the problem into the manufacture of
a candidate alphabet, and that conversion is exact in both directions:
\textsc{SSG-Value} is in $\mathsf P$ \emph{if and only if} there is a
polynomial-time algorithm which, given $G$, outputs a set of rationals of
cardinality polynomial in $N$ containing every value of $G$. The forward
direction computes $\val$ outright: the threshold gadget of
\Cref{thm:compare-equivalence} lets the decision procedure compare
$\val(v)$ with any dyadic $\theta$, and a binary search over the
denominators of \Cref{lem:denominator-sharp} recovers each value in $O(a)$
calls; the converse is \Cref{thm:alphabet-iteration}. So the
reformulation belongs with \Cref{thm:compare-equivalence},
\Cref{thm:order-determines} and \Cref{thm:decide-one-bit} in
\Cref{sec:selection} and \Cref{sec:gap}: it is of the same strength as the target and
relocates the difficulty rather than reducing it. What it relocates it
\emph{to} is worth recording, because it is less than those theorems ask
for. A candidate alphabet is a set of numbers with no assignment of
letters to vertices; \Cref{thm:order-determines} needs the induced
preorder on $\Vavg$, and \Cref{rem:bsi} needs a guide ordering each Max
vertex's two successors. For stopping games, by \Cref{thm:alphabet-rigid} the candidate is
moreover pinned by $k-2$ midpoint equations, so the search space is a list
computable from $k$ alone. What is not known is any bound on $k$: by
\Cref{thm:alphabet-denominator} and the equality
$k=2+\log_2 D$ measured on every hard family of
\Cref{rem:alphabet-compare}, $k$ is $\Theta(N)$ on every hard family
built here --- a measurement on the families listed there, not a theorem, and
$\mathrm{FV}_2$ in this subsection has $k=4$ and is easy.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Width: the search is quasipolynomial, the tables are not}\label{sec:width}

Every polynomial class above is a colour condition on cycles
(\Cref{thm:kacyclic}), a count (\Cref{thm:few-avg,thm:few-escape,thm:bounded-components}),
a rate (\Cref{thm:escape-class}) or the answer (\Cref{thm:few-denominator});
none is a \emph{width} condition, and the hard families of this document
all have small width. This subsection, from the round-$15$ bounded-width
route, records what a tree decomposition buys: the dynamic programme that
stores exact response tables is exponential already at pathwidth four
(\Cref{rem:fold-width}), while the search that only queries the response at
grid points is quasipolynomial at every constant treewidth (\Cref{thm:qp})
and polynomial whenever a bounded set of vertices carries the whole
recursion (\Cref{thm:modulator}), which answers the question of
\Cref{rem:kacyclic}. Throughout, $H$ is a payoff game (\Cref{def:payoff}),
$n$ its number of non-terminals, $a$ its number of average vertices.

\begin{definition}[Width]\label{def:width}
The \emph{interaction graph} $\Gamma(H)$ is the undirected simple graph on
the non-terminals with an edge $\{u,v\}$ whenever one is a successor of the
other; $\operatorname{tw}(H)$ and $\operatorname{pw}(H)$ are the treewidth and pathwidth of $\Gamma(H)$.
Terminals are absorbing and never need separating; for an SSG, whose two
sinks are single vertices, counting them raises either parameter by at
most two (a payoff game with many terminals can gain more).
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Transfer to payoff games]\label{lem:payoff-transfer}
Let $H$ be a stopping payoff game with rational terminal payoffs of common
denominator $\Delta$ and $a$ average vertices. Then (a) the contraction
estimate of \Cref{thm:contraction} holds for $T_H$, $T_\sigma$ and
$T_{\sigma,\tau}$ with the same rate $1-2^{-a}$; (b) $H$ is determined
with positional optimal strategies, and the unique fixed point of $T_H$
is $\val_H$ (\Cref{thm:stopping-determinacy}); (c) the two-sided
comparison of \Cref{cor:comparison} holds: $T_H\hat x\le\hat x$ implies
