$P$-successor of $u$ of positive probability to lie in $A$. Hence $A$ is
closed under $P$, and it contains no sink because $\phi=0$ there and
$M>0$. But $G$ is stopping, so the chain $M_{\sigma',\tau}$ reaches a
sink from every vertex with probability $1$ --- a contradiction. Hence
$A$ meets $\Delta$.

Finally let $v\in A\cap\Delta$. Since $v\in\Vmax$ and
$\sigma(v)\ne\sigma'(v)$, using $x(v)=x(\sigma(v))$ and
$\phi(\sigma(v))\le M=\phi(v)$,
\[
  y(\sigma(v))=x(\sigma(v))+\phi(\sigma(v))\ \le\ x(v)+\phi(v)=y(v)=y(\sigma'(v)).\qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The peak sits on a switch that is made]\label{cor:peak-sharp}
In \Cref{thm:peak-law}, $A\cap S_\sigma\cap\Delta(\sigma,\sigma')\ne\emptyset$:
the maximum of $\val_{\sigma'}-\val_\sigma$ is attained at a vertex that is
strictly switchable at $\sigma$ and at which $\sigma'$ differs from
$\sigma$. Along an all-switches run, for $0\le t<t'\le L$ the maximum of
$\val_{\sigma_{t'}}-\val_{\sigma_t}$ is attained on $S_t$, at a vertex
that $\sigma_{t'}$ has moved; with $t=0$ and $t'=L$, the whole improvement
of a run is realised at a vertex strictly switchable at its first round.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
In the proof of \Cref{thm:peak-law}, if $v\in\Delta$ is not strictly
switchable at $\sigma$ then $\sigma'(v)=\bar\sigma(v)$ and
$\delta(v)=x(\bar\sigma(v))-x(\sigma(v))\le0$; so $\delta\le0$ off
$S_\sigma\cap\Delta$ and $\phi\le P\phi$ holds at every vertex outside
$S_\sigma\cap\Delta$. If $A$ missed $S_\sigma\cap\Delta$ the closedness
argument of the theorem would apply to $A$ verbatim and contradict
stopping. Along a run $\val_{\sigma_{t'}}\ge\val_{\sigma_t}$ with a strict
increase somewhere (\Cref{lem:switch}), and a vertex of $S_t$ at which
$\sigma_{t'}$ still agrees with $\sigma_t$ is outside
$\Delta(\sigma_t,\sigma_{t'})$. (Checked besides on $714$ run pairs of
$250$ random two-player stopping games in exact arithmetic; the weaker
form with $S_t$ alone is the round-$15$ lane route's, and its auditor
supplied this one.)
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Rise bound]\label{lem:rise-bound}
Let $G$ be stopping, let $\sigma,\sigma'$ be Max strategies with
$\val_{\sigma'}\ge\val_\sigma$, and let $u\in\Vmax$ be at rest at
$\sigma$, that is $u\notin S_\sigma$. Put $b:=\sigma'(u)$, let $(p,q)$ be
the first-passage law of the action $b$ onto $C\cup\{t_1\}$ --- $p_w$ the
probability that the token, leaving $u$ by $b$, next meets a controlled
vertex at $w$, and $q$ the probability that it reaches $t_1$ first; a
datum of the graph, since only average vertices lie between controlled
ones --- and let $W:=\{w\in C\setminus\{u\}:p_w>0\}$ be the controlled
vertices the action reads. Then
\[
  \val_{\sigma'}(u)-\val_\sigma(u)\ \le\ \max_{w\in W}\bigl(\val_{\sigma'}(w)-\val_\sigma(w)\bigr),
\]
the maximum over the empty set being $0$. In particular, if $W=\{w\}$ then
$\val_{\sigma'}(u)-\val_{\sigma'}(w)\le\val_\sigma(u)-\val_\sigma(w)$: a
vertex once at rest never overtakes the single controlled vertex its
later action reads.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Every value vector is a fixed point of its frozen operator, hence the mean
of its successors at every average vertex, so for every Max strategy
$\rho$ and $v\in\Vmax$ one has
$\val_\rho(v)=\sum_{w\in C}p^{v,\rho(v)}_w\val_\rho(w)+q^{v,\rho(v)}$ ---
the collapse of the average part that also underlies \Cref{thm:impedance}.
Write $x:=\val_\sigma$, $y:=\val_{\sigma'}$, $\Delta_w:=y(w)-x(w)\ge0$ and
$M:=\max_W\Delta_w$. At rest the action $b$ is worth at most $x(u)$ under
$x$, that is $p_ux(u)+\sum_{w\ne u}p_wx(w)+q\le x(u)$, while
$\sigma'(u)=b$ gives $y(u)=p_uy(u)+\sum_{w\ne u}p_wy(w)+q$. Subtracting,
$(1-p_u)\bigl(y(u)-x(u)\bigr)\le\sum_{w\ne u}p_w\Delta_w\le(1-p_u)M$,
the row being substochastic; and $p_u<1$, since otherwise $\{u\}$
together with the average vertices on its return paths would be a trap
(\Cref{lem:trapchar}). The last clause is the case $W=\{w\}$ rearranged.
(Verified besides on $671$ run pairs of $250$ random two-player stopping
games, every Max vertex at rest.)
\end{proof}

Now let $\sigma_0,\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_L$ be the all-switches run from
some $\sigma_0$, so $\sigma_{t+1}=\sigma_t[S_t]$ with
$S_t:=S_{\sigma_t}\ne\emptyset$ for $t<L$ and $S_{\sigma_L}=\emptyset$.
By \Cref{lem:switch} the value vectors increase strictly along it.

\begin{corollary}[Backward law]\label{cor:no-return}
For all $0\le t<t'\le L$ we have
$\Delta(\sigma_t,\sigma_{t'})\cap S_t\ne\emptyset$; equivalently
$\sigma_{t'}$ and $\sigma_t$ differ somewhere on $S_t$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
$\val_{\sigma_{t'}}\ge\val_{\sigma_t}$ and the two differ, so
$\val_{\sigma_{t'}}\not\le\val_{\sigma_t}$; apply
\Cref{lem:monotone-law} and note that a vertex with
$\val_{\sigma_t}(\sigma_{t'}(v))>\val_{\sigma_t}(\sigma_t(v))$ lies in
$S_t$, since $\sigma_{t'}(v)$ is then the successor
$\bar\sigma_t(v)$.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}[No return to an isolated configuration]\label{cor:isolated}
Let $\sigma_0,\dots,\sigma_L$ be an all-switches run on a stopping SSG
with $S_t=\sigma_t\oplus\sigma_{t+1}$. If $S_t\subseteq W\subseteq\Vmax$
then $\sigma_{t'}|_W\ne\sigma_t|_W$ for every $t'>t$. In particular a
vertex that switches alone never switches again, and a sub-game on $W$
that runs in isolation for a single round is never reset to that local
configuration and re-run.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
\Cref{cor:no-return} gives $\Delta(\sigma_t,\sigma_{t'})\cap S_t\ne\emptyset$
and $S_t\subseteq W$.
\end{proof}


\begin{corollary}[Forward law]\label{cor:law-b}
For all $0\le t<t'\le L-1$ we have
$\Delta(\sigma_t,\sigma_{t'})\not\subseteq S_{t'}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
\Cref{thm:peak-law} with $\sigma=\sigma_t$, $\sigma'=\sigma_{t'}$
produces $v\in\Delta(\sigma_t,\sigma_{t'})$ not strictly switchable at
$\sigma_{t'}$, that is $v\notin S_{t'}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Antichain law]\label{cor:antichain}
For $0\le t\le L-2$, $S_t\not\subseteq S_{t+1}$; in particular
$S_t\ne S_{t+1}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Since $\sigma_{t+2}=\sigma_t[S_t\bigtriangleup S_{t+1}]$, the assumption
$S_t\subseteq S_{t+1}$ gives
$\Delta(\sigma_t,\sigma_{t+2})=S_{t+1}\setminus S_t$, which is disjoint
from $S_t$, contradicting \Cref{cor:no-return} with $t'=t+2$.
\end{proof}

These laws constrain the \emph{schedule} of switched sets. The next
theorem bounds the run outright, in terms of the graph alone.

\begin{definition}[Max-reachability digraph]\label{def:maxreach}
$H(G)$ has vertex set $\Vmax$ and an edge $u\to v$ whenever $v$ is
reachable from $u$ in $G$ along a directed path of positive length. Two
distinct Max vertices lie in the same strongly connected component of
$H(G)$ exactly when each is reachable from the other in $G$ --- a weaker
condition than lying on a common cycle: with $u\to(c,t_0)$, $v\to(d,t_0)$,
$c\to(a,t_0)$, $d\to(a,t_0)$ and $a\to(u,v)$ the Max vertices $u,v$ are
mutually reachable, yet no cycle passes through both.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Component bound]\label{thm:component-bound}
Let $G$ be stopping and let $C_1,\dots,C_r$ be the strongly connected
components of $H(G)$. Then from every initial strategy the all-switches
run halts after at most $\sum_{j}\bigl(2^{|C_j|}-1\bigr)$ iterations.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For a component $C$ let $\mathcal D(C)$ be the set of components
reachable from $C$ in the condensation of $H(G)$, $C$ included, and
$D(C)$ the union of its members; let $R(C)$ be $C$ together with all
vertices of $G$ reachable from $C$. Then $R(C)$ is
closed under successors and $\Vmax\cap R(C)=D(C)$, by
\Cref{def:maxreach}. Hence $R(C)$ carries a stopping sub-SSG, and
$\val_\sigma$ restricted to $R(C)$ is computed inside it and depends only
on $\sigma|_{D(C)}$.

The run halts, by \Cref{lem:switch} and finiteness. Let $t(C)$ be least
with $\sigma_s|_C=\sigma_{t(C)}|_C$ for all $s\ge t(C)$. We show
$t(C)\le\sum_{C'\in\mathcal D(C)}(2^{|C'|}-1)$ by induction along the
condensation, from its sinks upward. Put
$T:=\max\{t(C'):C'\ne C,\ C'\in\mathcal D(C)\}$, or $0$ if there is no such
$C'$. For $t\ge T$ the restriction $\sigma_t|_{D(C)\setminus C}$ is
constant, so $\val_{\sigma_t}|_{R(C)}=F(\sigma_t|_C)$ for a fixed map
$F$; and both successors of every vertex of $C$ lie in $R(C)$, so
$S_{\sigma_t}\cap C=g(\sigma_t|_C)$ for a fixed map $g$, whence
$\sigma_{t+1}|_C=\sigma_t|_C[g(\sigma_t|_C)]$ for all $t\ge T$: from time
$T$ on the restriction evolves autonomously and deterministically. Such a
sequence is eventually periodic, and being eventually constant it is
constant from its first repetition on; so
$\sigma_T|_C,\dots,\sigma_{t(C)}|_C$ are pairwise distinct and
$t(C)-T\le2^{|C|}-1$. Since the condensation is acyclic, $C\notin D(C')$
for every $C'\ne C$ in $\mathcal D(C)$, so $\mathcal D(C')\subseteq\mathcal D(C)\setminus\{C\}$ and
the induction closes. At time $\max_C t(C)$ every component is frozen, so
no Max vertex is strictly switchable and the run has ended.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Bounded Max-components]\label{thm:bounded-components}
For $k\ge1$ let $\mathcal K_k$ be the class of SSGs in which every
strongly connected component of $H(G)$ has at most $k$ vertices --- for
$k=1$, those in which no two distinct Max vertices are mutually reachable.
On a stopping game of $\mathcal K_k$ the all-switches run has length at
most $|\Vmax|(2^{k}-1)/k$, and \Cref{prob:main} restricted to
$\mathcal K_k$ is decidable in time $\mathrm{poly}(N)2^{k}$; for
$k=O(\log N)$ it is decidable in polynomial time. The class is closed
under \Cref{def:damping}, since subdividing an edge changes no
reachability among Max vertices, so the reduction of
\Cref{thm:stopping-transform} keeps the hypothesis.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Maximising $\sum_j(2^{|C_j|}-1)$ subject to $|C_j|\le k$ and
$\sum_j|C_j|=|\Vmax|$ puts every component at size $k$, giving
$(|\Vmax|/k)(2^{k}-1)$; now apply \Cref{thm:component-bound}. Each round
costs one Min best response and one evaluation of $\val_\sigma$,
polynomial by \Cref{thm:eval-stopfree}, and the run ends at an optimal
strategy by \Cref{lem:local-global}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[$\mathcal K_1$ escapes the four combinatorial classes of
\Cref{sec:special}]\label{prop:k1-family}
For $n\ge1$ let $K_n$ be the SSG with blocks $i=1,\dots,n$ consisting of
$x_i\in\Vmax$, $m_i\in\Vmin$ and $c_i,d_i,e_i\in\Vavg$, and edges
\[
  x_i\to(c_i,e_i),\quad c_i\to(m_i,d_i),\quad m_i\to(x_i,d_i),\quad
  d_i\to(e_i,x_{i+1}),\quad e_i\to(t_1,t_0),
\]
where $x_{n+1}:=t_1$. Then $K_n$ is stopping with $N=5n+2$,
$a=3n$, $|\Vmax|=|\Vmin|=n$; every component of $H(K_n)$ is a singleton,
so $K_n\in\mathcal K_1$ and all-switches ends within $n$ rounds from any
start, a bound attained for $n\le7$; and $w^\ast(x_1)=\tfrac12+2^{-(n+1)}$.
$K_n$ lies in the escape class of \Cref{thm:escape-class}, with the
certificate $\lambda=\tfrac{19}{20}$ and $x=3$ at $x_i$, $\tfrac{60}{19}$
at $m_i$, $\tfrac{1000}{361}$ at $c_i$, $\tfrac{40}{19}$ at $d_i$, $1$ at
$e_i$, whose conditioning is constant in $n$ (verified for $n\le40$), so
that theorem solves it in $O(N)$ iterations. Yet
$K_n$ lies in none of the four combinatorial classes of \Cref{sec:special}: the cycle
$x_i\to c_i\to m_i\to x_i$ carries an average vertex and two controlled
vertices, so neither \Cref{thm:avg-acyclic} nor \Cref{thm:player-free}
applies; $\Vmin\ne\emptyset$, so \Cref{thm:one-player} does not; and
$a=3(N-2)/5$, so \Cref{thm:few-avg} gives only an exponential bound.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Stopping: a trap contains no sink, so it omits every $e_i$; then it omits
every $d_i$, which needs $e_i$; then every $c_i$, which needs $d_i$; then
every $x_i$, whose successors are $c_i,e_i$; then every $m_i$, whose
successors are $x_i,d_i$. So the only trap is empty
(\Cref{lem:trapchar}). Every edge leaving block $i$ goes to $x_{i+1}$ or
to a sink, so $H(K_n)$ is acyclic on $x_1,\dots,x_n$ with every edge
directed forward, and its components are singletons. The value: with
$E_i=\tfrac12$, $D_i=\tfrac12(E_i+V_{i+1})$, $M_i=\min(V_i,D_i)$,
$C_i=\tfrac12(M_i+D_i)$, $V_i=\max(C_i,E_i)$ and $V_{n+1}=1$, the vector
$V_i=D_i=M_i=C_i=\tfrac12+2^{-(n-i+2)}$, $E_i=\tfrac12$ is a fixed point
of $T$, the unique one since $K_n$ is stopping, so
$w^\ast(x_1)=\tfrac12+2^{-(n+1)}$. The escape certificate is checked by
substitution ($Sx=\lambda x$ at $m_i,c_i,d_i$, $Sx<\lambda x$ at $x_i,e_i$).
The run lengths from all $2^{n}$ starting strategies are exact
computations for $n\le7$; that the bound $n$ is attained for every $n$ is
not proved. The four non-memberships are read off the edges.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[A ratio $3/2$ overshoot on four Max
vertices]\label{prop:overshoot-small}
Let $B'$ be the game on non-sink vertices $0,\dots,11$ with $t_0=12$,
$t_1=13$, types $(\max,\max,\max,\max,\mathrm{avg},\dots,\mathrm{avg})$,
no Min vertices, and out-edge pairs, in vertex order,
\[
 (4,3),(9,2),(3,1),(2,8),(1,13),(12,13),(9,9),(2,8),(3,10),(2,0),(11,8),(5,13),
\]
and let $G':=B'_7$ be its damping with $m=7$, a stopping SSG on $182$
vertices with the same four Max vertices. From
$\sigma_0=(1,1,1,0)$ the all-switches rule performs $6=\tfrac32|\Vmax|$
iterations, switching $\{0,3\},\{0,1,2\},\{0,1\},\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}$ and
ending with $\val(0)=34611559809/37019025280$. This improves the ratio
$16/14$ of \Cref{prop:allswitch-overshoot} on an instance two to four
times smaller ($182$ vertices and four Max vertices against $422$ and
fourteen).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A finite exact computation. As in \Cref{prop:allswitch-overshoot} there
are no Min vertices, so each $\val_\sigma$ is one rational linear solve.
Both the explicit $180$-non-sink-vertex damping and the reduced
$12$-variable system in which each edge delivers its head with
probability $1-2^{-7}$ and $t_0$ otherwise were solved, with identical
values at all twelve original vertices at every step. The value vectors
increase strictly, and the six switched sets satisfy
\Cref{cor:no-return,cor:law-b,cor:antichain}.
\end{proof}
\begin{proposition}[Damping cannot remove a tie at value zero]\label{prop:zero-ties}
Let $G^{\flat}$ be obtained from an SSG $G$ by replacing each edge $u\to w$
by a damping chain of \Cref{def:damping} of arbitrary, edge-dependent
length $k_e\ge0$, delivering $w$ with probability $1-2^{-k_e}$ and $t_0$
otherwise. Then for every Max strategy $\sigma$ the zero set of
$\val^{G^{\flat}}_\sigma$ on $V(G)$ is the zero set $Z_\sigma$ of
$\val^{G}_\sigma$, the greatest $\sigma$-trap of \Cref{def:trap}, which
depends on no probability; so every incidence tied at the value $0$ in
$G$ stays tied in $G^{\flat}$, and no such perturbation makes the damped
game $B'_7$ of \Cref{prop:overshoot-small} nondegenerate, all ten of its
ties being ties at $0$ ($B'$ itself has $26$ tied incidences: ten at $0$,
eleven at $\tfrac34$ and five at $1$).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By \Cref{lem:trap} the zero set of $\val_\sigma$ is the greatest
$\sigma$-trap, so it suffices to show that the greatest $\sigma$-trap
$Z^{\flat}$ of $G^{\flat}$ meets $V(G)$ in $Z$. Adjoining to $Z$ the chain
vertices of every edge whose head lies in $Z$ gives a $\sigma$-trap of
$G^{\flat}$ ($t_0\in Z$, and a chain vertex's two successors are the head
and the next chain vertex or $t_0$), so $Z\subseteq Z^{\flat}$; conversely
$Z^{\flat}\cap V(G)$ is a $\sigma$-trap of $G$, since an average vertex of
a trap keeps both successors, so a chain in $Z^{\flat}$ carries its head
into $Z^{\flat}$. A tie at $0$ has both heads in $Z$, and both chain heads
then carry the value $0$ in $G^{\flat}$.
\end{proof}

The run of $B'$ nonetheless has a nondegenerate realisation: its
orientation class is one of the $56$ Holt--Klee classes of height $6$
that \Cref{rem:hk-survey} realises by nondegenerate one-player games on
about a hundred vertices, and the round-$15$ verification route built one
on $253$ vertices --- each edge replaced by a chain leaking $2^{-7}$ to
$t_0$ and a chosen $2^{-j}$ to $t_1$ --- with the same run
$\{0,3\},\{0,1,2\},\{0,1\},\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}$ from the same start (exact,
re-verified by its auditor; that game is not recorded in this document or
its repository).


\begin{remark}\label{rem:allsw-laws}
The three laws are genuine constraints and they are not enough. Write
$f(m)$ for the greatest $L$ such that some sequence
$\sigma_0,\dots,\sigma_L$ in $\{0,1\}^{m}$, with $S_t:=\sigma_t\oplus
\sigma_{t+1}\ne\emptyset$, satisfies \Cref{cor:no-return} and
\Cref{cor:law-b}. Exhaustive search gives $f(m)=1,2,4,7,13$ for
$m=1,\dots,5$, whereas explicit games realise runs of length $4$ at $|\Vmax|=3$ (the
$58$-vertex game of \Cref{rem:blowup-measured}), $6$ at $|\Vmax|=4$ with
one player (\Cref{prop:overshoot-small}) and $7$ at $|\Vmax|=4$ with two
(\Cref{prop:auso-seven}); by \Cref{cor:ceiling-general} the true maxima at
$m=3,4$ are $4$ and $7$. So the laws already forbid most schedules --- $f(5)=13$ against
the $2^{5}-1=31$ steps a bare counter would need --- but they do not by
themselves give a subexponential bound, and no argument here rules out a
schedule of exponential length obeying all three. \Cref{lem:auso-laws,cor:f-auso}
settle that: every bottom-antipodal trace of an acyclic unique sink orientation obeys
both laws, so $f$ is bounded below by the greatest bottom-antipodal height
$h^{\ast}$ of such an orientation, which is exponential by \Cref{thm:blowup}. The two
quantities are not equal --- \Cref{prop:hstar-five} gives $f(5)=13$ against
$h^{\ast}(5)=12$ --- so the laws are strictly weaker than the axioms they come
from. What
\Cref{thm:component-bound} shows is that any such schedule must live
inside a single large component of $H(G)$: an exponential all-switches
run needs $\Omega(N)$ Max vertices pairwise mutually reachable, and a
superpolynomial one $\omega(\log N)$ of them, and
that is a condition on the graph rather than on the schedule.
\end{remark}


\Cref{rem:allsw-laws} asks whether further laws could push $f$ down to a
polynomial. They cannot, and the reason is that the laws already capture
exactly one thing: the axioms of an acyclic unique sink orientation.

\begin{definition}[The improvement orientation]\label{def:improvement-uso}
Let $G$ be stopping with $\Vmax=\{x_1,\dots,x_m\}$, and identify Max strategies
with $\{0,1\}^{m}$. Call $G$ \emph{nondegenerate} if
$\val_\sigma(x_i^{(0)})\ne\val_\sigma(x_i^{(1)})$ for every $\sigma$ and every
$i$. The \emph{improvement outmap} is
\[
  s_G(\sigma)\;:=\;\{\,i:\ x_i\text{ is strictly switchable at }\sigma\,\}
  \;\subseteq\;\{1,\dots,m\},
\]
and the all-switches step is $\sigma\mapsto\sigma\oplus s_G(\sigma)$. An
orientation of the $m$-cube given by an outmap $s$ is a \emph{unique sink
orientation} if $(s(u)\oplus s(v))\cap(u\oplus v)\ne\emptyset$ for all $u\ne v$,
and \emph{acyclic} (an AUSO) if the digraph $u\to u\oplus e_i$, $i\in s(u)$, has
no directed cycle. The walk $\sigma\mapsto\sigma\oplus s(\sigma)$ on an
orientation is the \emph{bottom-antipodal} walk; on an acyclic orientation
it terminates --- in the face spanned by $u$ and $u\oplus s(u)$ the
restriction of $s$ is a unique sink orientation with source $u$, its
coordinatewise complement is again a unique sink orientation (the
condition is invariant under complementing $s$) and acyclic (every edge
reversed), so its sink $u$ is reachable from every vertex of the face,
and reversing that path is an $s$-path from $u$ to $u\oplus s(u)$; the
walk therefore strictly ascends the acyclic reachability order and
repeats no vertex --- and $h(v)$ denotes the number of its steps from the start $v$
--- so that $h$ drops by exactly one per step --- and
$h(s):=\max_vh(v)$ is the greatest number of steps from any start.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Face sinks and the outmap condition]\label{lem:sw}
An orientation $s$ of the $m$-cube satisfies
$(s(u)\oplus s(v))\cap(u\oplus v)\ne\emptyset$ for all $u\ne v$ if and only
if every face has exactly one sink. (Szab\'o and Welzl; from memory,
unchecked against the source.)
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $F$ be a face with direction set $J$. If the condition holds then
$u\mapsto s(u)\cap J$ is injective on $F$, hence a bijection onto the
subsets of $J$, and the vertex mapped to $\emptyset$ is the unique sink of
$F$. Conversely let every face have exactly one sink. Reversing a single
direction $i$ preserves this property: a face without direction $i$ is
unchanged, and if $i\in J$ then the sinks of $F$'s two $i$-facets, $w_0$
and $w_1$, are the only vertices of $F$ with no outgoing edge in a
direction of $J\setminus\{i\}$, before and after the reversal; before it,
exactly one of them has $i\notin s(w)$ (both would give $F$ two sinks,
neither would give it none), so after it exactly one has, namely the
other. Hence reversing any set $A$ of directions preserves the property,
and a vertex $u$ of $F$ with $s(u)\cap J=A$ is the unique sink of $F$ after
reversing $A$; so $u\mapsto s(u)\cap J$ is injective on $F$. For $u\ne v$
take $F$ the face spanned by $u$ and $v$, so that $J=u\oplus v$:
injectivity gives $s(u)\cap J\ne s(v)\cap J$, which is the condition.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[All-switches is a bottom-antipodal walk on an AUSO]\label{prop:allsw-auso}
For a nondegenerate stopping $G$, $s_G$ is the outmap of an AUSO of the
$m$-cube, and the all-switches run from $\sigma$ is exactly its
bottom-antipodal walk from $\sigma$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Unique sink: for $C\subseteq\{1,\dots,m\}$ and $\sigma$, the subgame
$G_C$ of \Cref{def:subcube} obtained by freezing the coordinates outside $C$ is
stopping (\Cref{lem:subgame}(a)), and by \Cref{thm:opt-subcube} its optimal
strategies form the subcube of greedy ones, which is nonempty; a point of the
face with no strictly switchable free coordinate is exactly a sink of that face
(\Cref{lem:subgame}(e)), and it is unique: the optimal strategies of $G_C$
are the greedy ones (\Cref{thm:opt-subcube}), and since
$w^\ast_{G_C}=\val_{\pi^\ast}$ for a Max strategy $\pi^\ast$ of $G$
(\Cref{lem:subgame}(b),(c)), nondegeneracy makes the two options of every
free coordinate unequal there, so the greedy subcube is a single point.
That every face has a unique sink is the outmap condition of
\Cref{def:improvement-uso} by \Cref{lem:sw}. Acyclicity:
along a strictly improving switch $\val_\sigma$ increases strictly in at least
one coordinate and nowhere decreases (\Cref{lem:switch}), so
$\sum_v\val_\sigma(v)$ strictly increases along every oriented edge and is a
linear extension of the reachability order. The second sentence is
\Cref{def:improvement-uso} read twice.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Every bottom-antipodal trace obeys the two laws]\label{lem:auso-laws}
Let $s$ be the outmap of an AUSO of the $m$-cube and let
$\sigma_0,\dots,\sigma_L$ be a bottom-antipodal trace, $S_t:=s(\sigma_t)$
nonempty for $t<L$ and $s(\sigma_L)=\emptyset$. Then the $\sigma_t$ are pairwise
distinct and, for all $0\le t<t'\le L$,
\[
  (\sigma_t\oplus\sigma_{t'})\cap S_t\ne\emptyset,
  \qquad\text{and for }t'\le L-1,\quad
  (\sigma_t\oplus\sigma_{t'})\not\subseteq S_{t'} .
\]
That is, every bottom-antipodal trace of every AUSO satisfies
\Cref{cor:no-return} and \Cref{cor:law-b}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Acyclicity gives a linear extension $\varphi$ of the reachability order, strictly
increasing along every oriented edge. On any face the $\varphi$-maximum has all
the face's edges incoming, so it is a sink of that face; dually the
$\varphi$-minimum is a source. Both are unique: replacing $s$ by its
coordinatewise complement reverses every edge and leaves
$(s(u)\oplus s(v))\cap(u\oplus v)$ unchanged, so the reversed orientation is
again a unique sink orientation, and its sinks are the original's sources. Let $F_t$ be the face through $\sigma_t$
spanned by $S_t$ and $H_t$ the face spanned by the complementary directions. All
edges at $\sigma_t$ inside $F_t$ point out, so $\sigma_t$ is the source of $F_t$;
all edges at $\sigma_t$ inside $H_t$ point in, so $\sigma_t$ is the sink of
$H_t$. Since $\sigma_{t+1}\in F_t$ differs from $\sigma_t$, $\varphi$ increases
strictly along the trace, so the $\sigma_t$ are distinct. For $t<t'$ we get
$\varphi(\sigma_{t'})>\varphi(\sigma_t)$, so $\sigma_{t'}\notin H_t$, which is
the first display. If $t'\le L-1$ and $\sigma_t\oplus\sigma_{t'}\subseteq
S_{t'}$ then $\sigma_t\in F_{t'}$ and $\sigma_t\ne\sigma_{t'}$, so
$\varphi(\sigma_t)>\varphi(\sigma_{t'})$, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\medskip
The nondegeneracy hypothesis of \Cref{prop:allsw-auso} can be deleted. That
matters, because the longest all-switches runs recorded in this document ---
\Cref{prop:overshoot-small} and \Cref{prop:allswitch-overshoot} --- are
heavily degenerate, so the AUSO ceiling $h^{\ast}(m)$ of
\Cref{cor:f-auso} did not, as things stood, apply to them.

\begin{definition}[Flat coordinates and flat classes]\label{def:flat}
For a stopping $G$ and a Max strategy $\sigma$ put, alongside
$S(\sigma):=S_\sigma$,
\[
  D(\sigma):=\bigl\{i:\val_\sigma(\bar\sigma(x_i))=\val_\sigma(\sigma(x_i))\bigr\},
\]
the \emph{flat} coordinates. Call the cube edge $\{\sigma,\sigma\oplus e_i\}$
\emph{flat} when $i\in D(\sigma)$, and let the \emph{flat classes} be the
connected components of the graph of flat edges.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Trichotomy: no edge points out at both ends]\label{lem:trichotomy}
Let $G$ be stopping, $\sigma$ a Max strategy, $i$ a coordinate and
$\sigma':=\sigma\oplus e_i$. Then exactly one of
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item $i\in S(\sigma)$, and then $i\notin S(\sigma')\cup D(\sigma')$;
\item $i\in S(\sigma')$, and then $i\notin S(\sigma)\cup D(\sigma)$;
\item $i\in D(\sigma)\cap D(\sigma')$, and then $\val_{\sigma'}=\val_\sigma$.
\end{enumerate}
In particular an edge is flat at one end if and only if it is flat at the
other, and the two ends of a flat edge carry the same value vector.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $a:=\sigma(x_i)$, $b:=\bar\sigma(x_i)=\sigma'(x_i)$, $x:=\val_\sigma$,
$y:=\val_{\sigma'}$, and split on the sign of $x(b)-x(a)$.

If $x(b)=x(a)$ then $T_{\sigma'}x=x$: the two operators differ only at $x_i$,
where $(T_{\sigma'}x)(x_i)=x(b)=x(a)=x(x_i)$ by \Cref{thm:lfp-general}(a).
By \Cref{thm:contraction} the fixed point of $T_{\sigma'}$ is unique, so
$y=x$; then $y(a)=x(a)=x(b)=y(b)$, so $i\in D(\sigma)\cap D(\sigma')$ and we
are in case (c).

If $x(b)>x(a)$ then $i\in S(\sigma)$. Suppose $y(a)\ge y(b)$. The operators
$T_\sigma$ and $T_{\sigma'}$ differ only at $x_i$, where
$(T_\sigma y)(x_i)=y(a)\ge y(b)=y(x_i)$, so $T_\sigma y\ge y$ and
\Cref{cor:comparison} gives $y\le\val_\sigma=x$. But $i\in S(\sigma)$ and
\Cref{lem:switch} give $y\ge x$ with strict inequality at $x_i$, a
contradiction. Hence $y(b)>y(a)$, that is $i\notin S(\sigma')\cup D(\sigma')$:
case (a). The case $x(b)<x(a)$ is the same with the roles of $\sigma$ and
$\sigma'$ exchanged, and gives (b).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The flat classes are the level sets]\label{lem:flat-class}
Let $G$ be stopping.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item $S$ and $D$ are constant on each flat class, and the class of $\sigma$
      is the subcube $\sigma\oplus\operatorname{span}\{e_i:i\in D(\sigma)\}$.
\item $\val_\sigma=\val_{\sigma'}$ if and only if $\sigma$ and $\sigma'$ lie
      in the same flat class: the flat classes are exactly the level sets of
      $\sigma\mapsto\val_\sigma$.
\item $\Phi(\val_\sigma):=\sum_v\val_\sigma(v)$ is constant on classes and
      strictly increases along every non-flat edge in the direction it
      points, so the quotient digraph on classes is acyclic and the
      strategies of an all-switches run lie in pairwise distinct classes.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) If $i\in D(\sigma)$ then $\val_{\sigma\oplus e_i}=\val_\sigma$ by
\Cref{lem:trichotomy}(c). For $j\ne i$ the two successors of $x_j$ and the
choice made there are the same at $\sigma$ and $\sigma\oplus e_i$, and the
value vector is the same, so $j\in S(\sigma)\Leftrightarrow j\in S(\sigma\oplus e_i)$
and likewise for $D$; and $i\in D(\sigma\oplus e_i)$ by the same trichotomy.
So $S$ and $D$ are unchanged by a flat flip, and the class is obtained by
flipping the coordinates of the constant set $D$ in all ways.

(b) One direction is (a). Conversely let $\val_\sigma=\val_{\sigma'}$ and let
$i$ be a coordinate where they differ. By \Cref{thm:lfp-general}(a),
$\val_\sigma(x_i)=\val_\sigma(\sigma(x_i))$ and
$\val_{\sigma'}(x_i)=\val_{\sigma'}(\sigma'(x_i))$; the two left sides agree
and $\val_{\sigma'}=\val_\sigma$, so $\val_\sigma$ takes the same value at
both successors of $x_i$, that is $i\in D(\sigma)$. Every differing
coordinate is flat, so $\sigma'$ lies in the class of $\sigma$ by (a).

(c) Constancy is (b). The strict increase is \Cref{lem:switch}; the run's
strategies therefore have strictly increasing $\Phi$ and lie in distinct
classes.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Every face has a sink subcube]\label{lem:face-sink}
Let $G$ be stopping and let $F$ be a face of the $m$-cube with free direction
set $J$. Then $\{\sigma\in F:S(\sigma)\cap J=\emptyset\}$ is nonempty, equals
$\{\sigma\in F:\val_\sigma=\max_{F}\val\}$, and is $A\cap F$ for a single
flat class $A$; it is a subcube of $F$ with direction set $D_A\cap J$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Freezing the coordinates outside $J$ gives the subgame $G_F$ of
\Cref{def:subcube}, which is stopping by \Cref{lem:subgame}(a), and by
\Cref{lem:subgame}(e) a point of $F$ with no strictly switchable free
coordinate is exactly a strategy of $G_F$ with no strictly switchable
vertex, i.e.\ an optimal one (\Cref{lem:local-global}). By
\Cref{thm:opt-subcube} those form a nonempty subcube, and they all carry the
same value vector, namely that of $G_F$; by \Cref{lem:flat-class}(b) they
therefore lie in one flat class $A$, and conversely every member of $A\cap F$
has the same $S$, hence is also a sink of $F$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Resolution]\label{thm:flat-resolution}
Let $G$ be stopping with $|\Vmax|=m$, and choose a corner $c_A$ in each flat
class $A$. Then
\[
  s'(\sigma)\;:=\;S(\sigma)\;\cup\;\bigl\{i\in D(\sigma):\sigma_i\ne(c_{A(\sigma)})_i\bigr\}
\]
is the outmap of an acyclic unique sink orientation of the $m$-cube, and
$s'(c_A)=S(c_A)$ for every class $A$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Orientation.} Let $\{\sigma,\sigma'\}$ be an edge in direction $i$. In
cases (a) and (b) of \Cref{lem:trichotomy} the coordinate $i$ is flat at
neither end, so $s'$ inherits $S$'s single orientation. In case (c) both ends
lie in one class $A$ and differ at $i$, so exactly one of them disagrees with
$c_A$ at $i$ and receives the direction.

\emph{Unique sink.} Let $F$ be a face with free set $J$. By
\Cref{lem:face-sink} the $S$-sinks of $F$ are $A\cap F$ for one class $A$, a
subcube with direction set $D_A\cap J$, on which $S$ and $D$ are constant.
For $\sigma\in F$ we have $s'(\sigma)\cap J=\emptyset$ exactly when
$S(\sigma)\cap J=\emptyset$ --- i.e.\ $\sigma\in A\cap F$ --- and $\sigma$
agrees with $c_A$ on $D_A\cap J$. All members of $A$ agree off $D_A$, so
$c_A$ does too, and $A\cap F$ contains exactly one point agreeing with $c_A$
on $D_A\cap J$. So every face has exactly one sink, which is the outmap
condition by \Cref{lem:sw}.

\emph{Acyclicity.} By \Cref{lem:flat-class}(c) the quotient digraph on classes
is acyclic; fix a topological order $\psi$ of it. Along an $s'$-arc that is
not flat, the class strictly increases in $\psi$; along a flat one the class
is unchanged and the Hamming distance to $c_A$ drops by one, since the arc
points from disagreement to agreement. So
$\bigl(\psi(A(\sigma)),-\mathrm{dist}(\sigma,c_A)\bigr)$ increases
lexicographically along every arc.

Finally $c_A$ disagrees with itself nowhere, so $s'(c_A)=S(c_A)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The ceiling holds for degenerate games too]\label{cor:ceiling-general}
For every stopping SSG with $|\Vmax|=m$, degenerate or not, and every
starting strategy, the all-switches run $\sigma_0,\dots,\sigma_L$ is the
bottom-antipodal walk from $\sigma_0$ of an AUSO of the $m$-cube. Hence
$L\le h^{\ast}(m)$, and in particular $L\le1,2,4,7,12$ for $m\le5$: no
stopping SSG with five Max vertices has an all-switches run of length $13$,
although \Cref{cor:no-return} and \Cref{cor:law-b} permit such schedules.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By \Cref{lem:flat-class}(c) the $\sigma_t$ lie in pairwise distinct classes,
so setting $c_{A(\sigma_t)}:=\sigma_t$ is a consistent choice of corners;
extend it arbitrarily to the remaining classes and apply
\Cref{thm:flat-resolution}. Then $s'(\sigma_t)=S(\sigma_t)=S_t$ for $t<L$ and
$s'(\sigma_L)=\emptyset$, so the $s'$-bottom-antipodal walk from $\sigma_0$ is
the run. The numerical ceiling is \Cref{prop:auso-census} and
\Cref{prop:hstar-five}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[A third law]\label{cor:law-u}
Along every all-switches run of every stopping SSG, with $S_L:=\emptyset$,
\[
  (S_t\,\triangle\,S_{t'})\cap(\sigma_t\oplus\sigma_{t'})\ \ne\ \emptyset
  \qquad(0\le t<t'\le L).
\]
Consequently the switched sets $S_0,\dots,S_L$ are pairwise distinct; and
the display itself is strictly stronger than \Cref{cor:antichain}, which
is its case $t'=t+1$ read as $S_t\setminus S_{t+1}\ne\emptyset$, and which
it also covers at $t=L-1$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The display is the defining condition of a unique sink orientation applied to
the two distinct vertices $\sigma_t,\sigma_{t'}$ of the AUSO supplied by
\Cref{cor:ceiling-general}, whose outmap agrees with $S_t$ along the run. If
$S_t=S_{t'}$ the symmetric difference is empty and the condition fails.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:flat}
Three consequences. First, \Cref{prop:allsw-auso}'s nondegeneracy hypothesis
is no longer load-bearing anywhere: degenerate combinatorics is not a
separate object, and the search for a superpolynomial family may be conducted
among AUSOs without loss. Second, \Cref{cor:f-auso}'s cap now applies to
every stopping game, so \emph{any} improvement on the ceiling must use a
property that SSG-realisable orientations have and general AUSOs lack ---
which is what \Cref{prop:auso-size} says cannot be found by enumeration.
Third, \Cref{cor:law-u} tightens the law programme of
\Cref{rem:allsw-laws}: adding it to \Cref{cor:no-return} and
\Cref{cor:law-b}, exhaustive search reports the ceiling
$1,2,4,7,12,21$ at $m\le6$ in place of $f=1,2,4,7,13,25$, so at $m=5$ the
law bound now equals $h^{\ast}(5)=12$ exactly. The $m=5$ entries were
reproduced here; the two $m=6$ entries are recorded as measurements ---
$f(6)=25$ is the repository's exhaustive count, and $21$ was obtained by
two independent exhaustive searches (round $14$'s and a later verification
route's), the repository's own committed outputs recording only a run
under a different set of laws that reached $20$;
everything else in this subsection is proved.

What this does not do should be said plainly. It yields no bound on any
algorithm, no polynomial class, no barrier and no lower-bound family, and
quantitatively it improves only the trivial ceiling $L\le2^{m}-1$ --- trivial
because $\Phi(\val_{\sigma})$ strictly increases along the run --- to
$h^{\ast}(m)$, which is itself at least $2^{m/2+1}-2$ (even $m$) or
$3\cdot2^{(m-1)/2}-2$ (odd $m$) by \Cref{thm:blowup}. Its value is
bookkeeping: it removes a hypothesis under which half of this document's
own instances fell, so that the pivot may be stated without one.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[The programme of \Cref{rem:allsw-laws} is capped]\label{cor:f-auso}
Let $h^{\ast}(m)$ be the greatest bottom-antipodal height of an AUSO of the
$m$-cube. Then $f(m)\ge h^{\ast}(m)$. Consequently no set of laws derivable from
the AUSO axioms alone can bound all-switches better than $h^{\ast}$, and any
improvement on $f$ must use a property of improvement orientations that not
every AUSO has.
\end{corollary}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:f-auso}
The bound is tight at the sizes we can enumerate: there are $12$ AUSOs of the
$2$-cube and $728$ of the $3$-cube, with greatest bottom-antipodal heights $2$
and $4$, against $f(2)=2$ and $f(3)=4$ from \Cref{rem:allsw-laws}. We verified
\Cref{lem:auso-laws} exhaustively at those sizes --- every bottom-antipodal
trace from every start of every one of those $740$ orientations, no violation of
either law --- and \Cref{prop:allsw-auso} on $200$ nondegenerate stopping games
with $2\le|\Vmax|\le4$ and $|\Vmin|\le3$: the improvement outmap was a unique
sink orientation and acyclic in every case, and every trace obeyed both laws.
Degeneracy is common and was skipped, $1236$ instances of it.

What \Cref{cor:f-auso} settles is the question \Cref{rem:allsw-laws} leaves
open. It is known that $h^{\ast}(m)$ grows exponentially: Schurr and Szab\'o
construct a blow-up that raises the dimension by two and at least doubles the
bottom-antipodal height. \Cref{thm:blowup} below proves a blow-up of that
kind from scratch, with the recurrence $h^{\ast}(m+2)\ge2h^{\ast}(m)+2$, so
that nothing is imported: iterating it from the one- and two-dimensional
seeds gives $h^{\ast}(m)\ge3\cdot2^{(m-1)/2}-2$ for odd $m$ and
$h^{\ast}(m)\ge2^{m/2+1}-2$ for even $m$. So $f$ is exponential and no
amount of further law-deriving can make the counting argument of
\Cref{rem:allsw-laws} subexponential. \Cref{thm:component-bound} is unaffected, since it constrains
the graph rather than the schedule.

\Cref{prop:allsw-auso} relocates the question. Its nondegeneracy hypothesis
looked at first like a real restriction, since the longest runs recorded in
this document are degenerate --- the witness of
\Cref{prop:overshoot-small} has, in its reduced twelve-variable model, ten
tied incidences $(\sigma,i)$ out of $64$ and hence five unoriented cube edges
out of $32$, and we recomputed all sixteen value vectors exactly to check it
--- and there a cube edge can be left unoriented, so the outmap is not that of
a unique sink orientation. \Cref{thm:flat-resolution} removes the restriction
entirely: such an outmap is still a \emph{flat} orientation, every flat
orientation resolves to an AUSO preserving any one prescribed run, and
\Cref{cor:ceiling-general} therefore reads every all-switches run of every
stopping game as a bottom-antipodal walk. So an exponential all-switches
family of stopping SSGs exists if and only if some stopping game realises an
AUSO of exponential bottom-antipodal height, with no hypothesis attached.

We had at one point recorded, as an observation, that hill climbing over
\emph{nondegenerate} stopping games did not exceed bottom-antipodal height $3$
at $|\Vmax|=3$, and asked whether the nondegenerate class is genuinely weaker.
It is not, at that size: the unique height-$4$ class of the $3$-cube is the
improvement orientation of a nondegenerate one-player stopping SSG. Searching
raw games is simply the wrong space --- the right one is the normal form of
\Cref{lem:transport-dim}'s harmonic reduction, in which a one-player game is a
family of $2m$ substochastic affine maps and the orientation is read off an
arrangement --- and this is one more instance of the rule that what is hard here
has to be engineered rather than sampled. We withdraw the observation.

What does obstruct the census, and cannot be searched away, is size.
\end{remark}


\begin{proposition}[The converse needs a size clause]\label{prop:auso-size}
A bottom-antipodal trace of an acyclic orientation visits distinct vertices,
so $h(s)\le2^{m}-1$ for every acyclic unique sink orientation of the $m$-cube
(on a general orientation the walk need not terminate). Hence an AUSO of height $2^{\Theta(m)}$ yields a
\emph{superpolynomial} all-switches lower bound only if it is realised by a
stopping SSG on $N=m^{O(1)}$ vertices. But at most
\[
  3^{N}\,(N+2)^{2N}\,N^{m}\;=\;2^{O(N\log N)}
\]
orientations of the $m$-cube arise as improvement orientations of stopping SSGs
on at most $N$ vertices, since such a game is determined by a kind for each of
$N$ vertices, an ordered successor pair for each, and a choice of which ordered
$m$-tuple carries the cube. The $m$-cube has $2^{m2^{m-1}}$ orientations in all.
So for $N$ polynomial in $m$ the realisable ones are a $2^{-\Omega(2^{m})}$
fraction.
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:auso-size}
\Cref{prop:auso-size} says the equivalence above is not size-preserving in the
direction that matters, and it explains why an exhaustive census cannot settle
the question. At $m=3$ and $m=4$ the number of orientations is far smaller than
the number of parameters a game may spend, and much is realisable --- at
$m=3$, sixteen of the eighteen isomorphism classes are Holt--Klee and hence
candidates for nondegenerate one-player realisation, the other two are not
(\Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp}), and at $m=4$ every Holt--Klee class of height
$6$ was realised by a one-player game (\Cref{rem:hk-survey}). At $m=\Theta(\log
N)$, which is where a superpolynomial run would have to live, the counting goes
the other way by an exponential margin. Enumeration will therefore never decide
this, and neither will sampling; only a construction will. The smallest
instance the counting leaves open --- whether the height-$7$ orbit at
$m=4$ is realised at all --- is settled affirmatively by
\Cref{prop:auso-seven} below, on $97$ vertices.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[The height-$7$ orientation is realised, by two
players]\label{prop:auso-seven}
There is a nondegenerate stopping SSG $G^{\sharp}$ on $97$ vertices --- four
Max, two Min, eighty-nine average and the two sinks --- whose improvement
outmap is
\[
  s_{G^{\sharp}}=(0,1,3,6,7,4,13,10,14,15,9,12,11,8,5,2),
\]
an acyclic unique sink orientation of the $4$-cube of bottom-antipodal
height $7=h^{\ast}(4)$, attained by the walk
$8\to6\to11\to7\to13\to5\to1\to0$. Writing $c_1,\dots,c_4$ for the Max and
$c_5,c_6$ for the Min vertices, the harmonic normal form of $G^{\sharp}$
--- the first-passage law of the average part from each of the twelve
actions, as a multiple of $\tfrac1{128}$ over the targets
$(c_1,\dots,c_6,t_1)$, the remaining mass going to $t_0$ --- is
\[
 \begin{array}{r@{\quad}l@{\qquad}l}
  c_1: & (6,3,88,1,0,0,4)      & (1,0,0,0,126,0,0)\\
  c_2: & (0,0,14,113,0,0,0)    & (0,6,0,0,0,0,53)\\
  c_3: & (0,0,0,127,0,0,0)     & (120,0,0,0,0,0,7)\\
  c_4: & (0,0,0,0,0,120,7)     & (2,74,46,0,0,0,0)\\
  c_5: & (0,0,0,0,13,0,64)     & (0,0,125,0,0,1,1)\\
  c_6: & (0,127,0,0,0,0,0)     & (0,0,121,0,0,1,4).
 \end{array}
\]
Every row sum is at most $127$, so every action leaks to $t_0$ and the game
is stopping. In particular $f(4)=7$ is attained by an actual game and not
merely permitted by the laws, and the orientation lies in the single free
orbit of $384$ outmaps of \Cref{prop:auso-census} that violates Holt--Klee.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
All assertions are finite exact computations on the game itself. Its
greatest trap is empty, so it is stopping by \Cref{lem:trapchar}. The
sixteen values $\val_\sigma$ were computed by taking, for each of the
sixteen Max strategies, the componentwise minimum over the four Min
strategies of the exact profile values; none of the $64$ incidences
$(\sigma,i)$ is tied, so $G^{\sharp}$ is nondegenerate in the sense of
\Cref{def:improvement-uso}, and the strictly switchable sets are the
outmap displayed. That outmap satisfies the condition of
\Cref{def:improvement-uso} and orients the cube acyclically, and its
bottom-antipodal walks have maximum length $7$, attained from exactly two
of the sixteen starts, $8$ and $9$; the sixteen lengths are
$0,1,2,3,4,2,6,4,7,7,4,5,5,3,6,4$. The first-passage laws of
the average part, computed by one linear solve per target with the
controlled vertices and the sinks made absorbing, agree with the displayed
table in all $84$ entries.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:auso-seven}
\Cref{rem:auso-size} named the realisability of the height-$7$ orbit at
$m=4$ as the smallest concrete instance of the question left open, and
\Cref{prop:auso-seven} settles it affirmatively. Two qualifications keep
it in proportion. It settles the instance, not the question: $h^{\ast}(4)=7$
is a constant, and what a superpolynomial run would need is a family, which
by \Cref{prop:auso-size} no census can supply. And the two players are doing real work, which
\Cref{cor:seven-two-player} below now establishes rather than assumes: the
orientation violates Holt--Klee, and \Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp} shows every
nondegenerate one-player improvement orientation is an LP orientation and
hence Holt--Klee. The obstacle noted here earlier --- that the natural proof
would need nondegeneracy of every game obtained by freezing Min --- does not
arise, because with $\Vmin$ empty there is nothing to freeze.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[One-player improvement orientations are LP orientations]\label{prop:oneplayer-lp}
Let $G$ be a nondegenerate stopping SSG with $\Vmin=\emptyset$ and
$\Vmax=\{x_1,\dots,x_m\}$, and let $(p^{v,a},q^{v,a})$ be its harmonic
normal form: the first-passage law of action $a$ at $v$ onto $\Vmax$ and
onto $t_1$. Put
\[
  X(G):=\Bigl\{y\in\mathbb R^{2m}_{\ge0}\ :\
    \textstyle\sum_a y_{w,a}-\sum_{v,a}p^{v,a}_{w}\,y_{v,a}=1\ \ (w\in\Vmax)\Bigr\},
  \qquad \mathrm{obj}(y):=\sum_{v,a}q^{v,a}y_{v,a}.
\]
Then the vertices of $X(G)$ are exactly the $2^{m}$ points $y^{\sigma}$
supported on a strategy, with
$y^{\sigma}_{v,\sigma(v)}=\bigl((I-P_\sigma^{\mathsf T})^{-1}\bbone\bigr)_v\ge1$;
$X(G)$ is a simple $m$-polytope whose graph is the $m$-cube and whose faces
are its subcubes; $\mathrm{obj}(y^{\sigma})=\sum_{v\in\Vmax}\val_\sigma(v)$; and
$\mathrm{obj}$ is non-constant on every edge, increasing from $y^{\sigma}$ to
$y^{\sigma[v]}$ exactly when $v\in S_\sigma$. Hence $s_G$ is the orientation
induced by a generic linear functional on a polytope combinatorially
equivalent to the $m$-cube, and is therefore a Holt--Klee orientation.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Every feasible $y$ has $\sum_a y_{w,a}\ge1$ for each $w$, the subtracted term
being nonnegative. A basic feasible solution of a system with $m$ equalities
has at most $m$ nonzero coordinates, so exactly one per $w$: it is supported
on a strategy $\sigma$, where the equations read
$(I-P_\sigma^{\mathsf T})y=\bbone$ with $(P_\sigma)_{v,w}:=p^{v,\sigma(v)}_w$.
Stopping makes $P_\sigma$ transient, so the inverse exists and is
$\sum_k P_\sigma^{k}\ge I$, giving $y^{\sigma}\ge\bbone$. Conversely each
$y^{\sigma}$ is feasible and has $m$ tight nonnegativity constraints, hence
is a nondegenerate vertex; relaxing the single constraint
$y_{v,\bar\sigma(v)}=0$ gives a one-dimensional face joining $y^{\sigma}$ to
$y^{\sigma[v]}$. So the graph is the $m$-cube and, faces being the sets with a
prescribed set of vanishing coordinates, the faces are its subcubes. The
polytope is bounded: a recession direction $d\ge0$ satisfies
$\sum_{v,a}(1-r_{v,a})d_{v,a}=0$ with $r_{v,a}$ the row sum of $p^{v,a}$,
so $d$ is supported on actions with $r_{v,a}=1$, and summing the recession
equations over $U:=\{w:\sum_ad_{w,a}>0\}$ shows those actions lead back
into $U$; a strategy taking one of them at every vertex of $U$ then never
leaves $U$, against stopping.

For the objective, $y^{\sigma}_{v}=\sum_w N_{w,v}$ with
$N:=(I-P_\sigma)^{-1}$ the expected number of visits, so
$\mathrm{obj}(y^{\sigma})=\sum_v q^{v,\sigma(v)}\sum_wN_{w,v}
=\sum_w\sum_vN_{w,v}q^{v,\sigma(v)}=\sum_{w\in\Vmax}\val_\sigma(w)$, the
sums over $v$ and $w$ running over $\Vmax$.
For the edges, \Cref{thm:impedance} expresses the change in $\val_\sigma$ at
$w$ caused by switching $v$ as
$(\val_\sigma(\bar\sigma(v))-\val_\sigma(\sigma(v)))\,h(w\!\to\!v)/(1-h(b\!\to\!v))$
with $h(v\!\to\!v)=1$, so every term has the sign of
$\val_\sigma(\bar\sigma(v))-\val_\sigma(\sigma(v))$, which is nonzero by
nondegeneracy; summing over $w$ gives the claim and shows $\mathrm{obj}$ is
generic. Holt--Klee's theorem applies to $X(G)$ and to each of its faces.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The height-seven orbit needs two players]\label{cor:seven-two-player}
No nondegenerate one-player stopping SSG has the improvement orientation of
\Cref{prop:auso-seven}, nor any orientation in its orbit. The two Min
vertices of $G^{\sharp}$ are therefore necessary, and the implication left
conditional in \Cref{rem:auso-seven} holds.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
That orientation violates Holt--Klee --- we verified this by a unit
vertex-capacity max-flow test on every face, which returns two
internally vertex-disjoint monotone source-to-sink paths on the whole
$4$-cube where four are required, a count confirmed by enumerating all
$42$ simple directed source-to-sink paths of that orientation and taking
a largest pairwise internally disjoint subfamily; already a $3$-face
(the one spanned by the coordinates $x_1,x_2,x_4$ at the vertex with
bitmask $4$, that is $x_3=1$) carries only two --- and
\Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp} makes every nondegenerate one-player improvement
orientation Holt--Klee. \Cref{thm:no-seven} below removes the
nondegeneracy hypothesis.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The two-player cube of $G^{\sharp}$]\label{prop:gsharp-bigcube}
Orient the $6$-cube of profiles $(\sigma,\tau)$ of $G^{\sharp}$ by letting a
controlled vertex be \emph{out} at a profile when its owner strictly prefers
its other action under the profile's value vector --- Max when the other
action has the larger value, Min when it has the smaller. Computed from the
normal form printed in \Cref{prop:auso-seven}, this orientation has no tie
at any of the $384$ incidences, is a unique sink orientation, is acyclic,
and satisfies Holt--Klee; its bottom-antipodal height is $5$. For each
$\sigma$ the face $\{\sigma\}\times\{0,1\}^{2}$ has a unique sink, at
Min's best response to $\sigma$, and the Max coordinates out at that sink
are exactly $s_{G^{\sharp}}(\sigma)$: the improvement orientation of the
Max cube is the sink projection of the two-player orientation. So the
projection destroys Holt--Klee, which the $6$-cube has and the $4$-cube
lacks (\Cref{cor:seven-two-player}), and raises the bottom-antipodal height
from $5$ to $7$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A computation in exact arithmetic: the $64$ profile values by one linear
solve each, the $384$ strict comparisons, the Szab\'o--Welzl condition on
all pairs, acyclicity by depth-first search, Holt--Klee by a unit
vertex-capacity maximum flow on each of the $729$ faces, and the projection
by locating the Min-face sinks. The rows are those of
\Cref{prop:auso-seven}; the Max-cube outmap recomputed from them agrees
with the one printed there.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:gsharp-bigcube}
The two-player orientation is the unique sink orientation that the
linear complementarity formulation of a stopping game induces on its
cube of complementary bases, after Stickney and Watson, and that such
orientations are Holt--Klee is the theorem of G\"artner, Morris and
R\"ust; we use neither fact, having checked the instance directly. What
the instance shows is the shape of the two-player half of the
realisability question: every improvement orientation of a stopping SSG
with Min vertices is the sink projection of a Holt--Klee unique sink
orientation of a larger cube, and the projection is where the height is
gained --- here two extra coordinates take a Holt--Klee orientation of
height $5$ to a non-Holt--Klee one of height $7$. At $m\le3$ every unique sink orientation, at $m=4$ the height-seven
orbit itself, and at $m=5$ the second blow-up level are such projections
with a \emph{single} extra coordinate (\Cref{sec:projection}), so
Holt--Klee imposes nothing on the projection at those sizes; which
projections a game actually realises is \Cref{rem:blowup-realise} in
another guise.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:oneplayer-lp}
\Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp} is a rediscovery and must be labelled one. $X(G)$ is
the classical occupancy, or dual, linear programme of a transient Markov
decision process, which goes back to d'Epenoux; that improvement orientations
arising from linear programmes are Holt--Klee is Holt and Klee's theorem
together with the observation of G\"artner, Morris and Ruest that realisable
unique sink orientations inherit it. The content added here is only the
identification of the SSG data with that programme and the resulting
\Cref{cor:seven-two-player}. Note also what the corollary does \emph{not}
say. Nondegeneracy is a genuine hypothesis: this document's longest
one-player all-switches runs are degenerate, and \Cref{thm:flat-resolution}
resolves those into AUSOs that need not be Holt--Klee, since the resolution
is a combinatorial completion and not an LP orientation. So the corollary, as proved here, bounds the heights of nondegenerate
one-player games; \Cref{sec:ties} shows that at $m=4$ the ceiling survives
degeneracy (\Cref{thm:no-seven}), by a different completion of the partial
orientation.
\end{remark}


\begin{proposition}[The census of the $m$-cube for $m\le4$]\label{prop:auso-census}
Enumerate the outmaps $s:\{0,1\}^{m}\to\{0,1\}^{m}$ satisfying the condition of
\Cref{def:improvement-uso}, that $(s(u)\oplus s(v))\cap(u\oplus v)\ne\emptyset$
for all $u\ne v$. For $m=2,3,4$ in turn there are
\[
  12,\qquad 744,\qquad 5541744
\]
unique sink orientations, of which $12$, $728$ and $4792176$ are acyclic,
falling into $2$, $18$ and $12640$ isomorphism classes under the automorphism
group $v\mapsto\pi(v)\oplus t$ of the cube, of order $2^{m}m!$. Every
bottom-antipodal walk of every one of those acyclic orientations terminates,
and
\[
  h^{\ast}(m)\;=\;1,\;2,\;4,\;7\qquad(m=1,2,3,4),
\]
the value $h^{\ast}(4)=7$ being attained by exactly $384$ outmaps, a single free
orbit. Say that an orientation is \emph{Holt--Klee} if every face of dimension
$d$ carries $d$ internally vertex-disjoint directed paths from its source to its
sink. Restricting to those leaves $12$, $656$ and $2322704$ acyclic
orientations, in $2$, $16$ and $6113$ classes, and
\[
  h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(m)\;=\;1,\;2,\;4,\;6\qquad(m=1,2,3,4).
\]
In particular no height-maximal orientation of the $4$-cube is Holt--Klee,
whereas on the $3$-cube the maximal height is attained by one that is.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The outmap of a unique sink orientation is injective, since
$(s(u)\oplus s(v))\cap(u\oplus v)\ne\emptyset$ fails when $s(u)=s(v)$; so it is
a bijection of $\{0,1\}^{m}$ and the orientations are enumerated by a
depth-first search that assigns $s(0),s(1),\dots$ in turn, testing the
displayed condition against the values already assigned. Acyclicity is a
depth-first search on the induced digraph, the bottom-antipodal height is
$\max_{\sigma}$ of the length of the walk $\sigma\mapsto\sigma\oplus s(\sigma)$,
isomorphism classes are counted by keeping only the orientation that is
lexicographically least in its orbit under the $2^{m}m!$ maps
$s'(\pi(v)\oplus t)=\pi(s(v))$, and the Holt--Klee test is a unit-capacity
maximum flow, with residual arcs, on the vertex-split graph of each face.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:auso-census}
The counts $744$ and $5541744$ of unique sink orientations of the $3$- and
$4$-cube agree with the published enumerations, which is the check on the
program; the rest of the table is what is used below.

Two consequences. First, \Cref{cor:f-auso} is tight one size further than
\Cref{rem:f-auso} records. \Cref{rem:allsw-laws} gives $f(m)=1,2,4,7$ for
$m\le4$ by exhaustive search over law-abiding schedules, and $h^{\ast}$ is the
same sequence, so for $m\le4$ the ceiling the two laws impose is exactly attained
by an acyclic unique sink orientation. The two do not agree in general:
\Cref{prop:hstar-five} separates them at $m=5$, the first size where that is
possible.

Second, the Holt--Klee count says where a height-maximal realisation must live.
By the theorem of Holt and Klee, the orientation induced on the graph of a
polytope by a generic linear objective admits $d$ vertex-disjoint monotone paths
between the two optima of every $d$-face; so every LP orientation is Holt--Klee,
and by \Cref{prop:auso-census} every LP orientation of the $4$-cube has
bottom-antipodal height at most $6$, against $7$ for orientations in general.
\Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp} proves that the improvement orientation of a nondegenerate one-player game is an LP orientation, so the pivot of \Cref{cor:f-auso} already separates the one-player from the two-player case at $m=4$, by exactly one step (\Cref{cor:seven-two-player}, \Cref{thm:no-seven}).
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[$h^{\ast}(5)=12<f(5)=13$]\label{prop:hstar-five}
On the $5$-cube there are exactly $22320$ sequences $\sigma_0,\dots,\sigma_{13}$
with $\sigma_0=0$ obeying \Cref{cor:no-return} and \Cref{cor:law-b}, so
$f(5)=13$; and none of them is the bottom-antipodal trace of an acyclic unique
sink orientation, while some sequence of length $12$ is. Hence
\[
  h^{\ast}(m)\;=\;1,\;2,\;4,\;7,\;12,\qquad
  f(m)\;=\;1,\;2,\;4,\;7,\;13\qquad(m=1,\dots,5),
\]
and the inequality $f\ge h^{\ast}$ of \Cref{cor:f-auso} is an equality for
$m\le4$ and strict at $m=5$. In particular the converse of
\Cref{lem:auso-laws} is false: the two laws do not characterise the
bottom-antipodal traces of acyclic unique sink orientations.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Both laws are invariant under translation of the cube, so $\sigma_0=0$ may be
assumed. A sequence is built left to right: choosing $S_t$ requires
$(\sigma_u\oplus\sigma_t)\not\subseteq S_t$ for every $u<t$, and setting
$\sigma_{t+1}:=\sigma_t\oplus S_t$ requires
$(\sigma_u\oplus\sigma_{t+1})\cap S_u\ne\emptyset$ for every $u\le t$; between
them these two tests cover every pair the two laws constrain, so the
enumeration is exhaustive. It gives
$31,750,12390,135930,967560,4378320,12280440,20388600,19136400,9748080,2550240,
347640,22320$ sequences of lengths $1,\dots,13$ and none of length $14$,
reproducing $f(5)=13$.

Realisability is then decided sequence by sequence. A sequence of length $L$ is
the bottom-antipodal trace of an orientation with outmap $s$ if and only if
$s(\sigma_t)=S_t$ for $t<L$ and $s(\sigma_L)=0$; since the outmap of a unique
sink orientation is injective, this already forces $L+1$ distinct values, and
the remaining $2^{m}-L-1$ are assigned by depth-first search subject to
$(s(u)\oplus s(v))\cap(u\oplus v)\ne\emptyset$, with acyclicity tested at each
completed assignment. For $L=13$ all $22320$ sequences were tried and none
completes. For $L=12$ a completion exists; the outmap
\[
  \begin{aligned}
  s=(&15,0,1,2,27,30,12,29,19,26,8,25,22,21,20,23,\\
     &31,18,28,17,3,6,24,5,7,14,16,13,10,9,4,11)
  \end{aligned}
\]
was checked independently to be a bijection, to satisfy the unique sink
condition, to have exactly one sink and one source on each of its faces by
direct inspection of all $211$ faces of positive dimension, to be acyclic, and to have
bottom-antipodal height exactly $12$, attained from $\sigma_0=0$ along
$0,15,24,31,20,23,18,14,26,10,2,3,1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:hstar-five}
The separation matters for what \Cref{cor:f-auso} does and does not say. It
still says that no bound derivable from the AUSO axioms alone can beat
$h^{\ast}$, so the programme of \Cref{rem:allsw-laws} cannot be pushed past that
ceiling. What it no longer says is that the two laws already exhaust those
axioms: at $m=5$ they admit a schedule that no acyclic unique sink orientation
performs, so there are further laws to be had, and the honest ceiling for a
law-based argument is $h^{\ast}$ and not $f$. Whether $h^{\ast}$ itself is
attained by improvement orientations of stopping games is the realisability
question of \Cref{prop:auso-size}, and is untouched by this.

One byproduct. The blow-up of \Cref{thm:blowup} raises the dimension by two
and takes the height $h$ to $2h+2$, so any exact value of $h^{\ast}$ is a seed
for it. Seeding at $m=4$ and at $m=5$ instead of at $m=1$ or $m=2$ gives
\[
  h^{\ast}(m)\;\ge\;9\cdot2^{(m-4)/2}-2\quad(m\ \text{even},\ m\ge4),
  \qquad
  h^{\ast}(m)\;\ge\;14\cdot2^{(m-5)/2}-2\quad(m\ \text{odd},\ m\ge5),
\]
that is $16,34,70,\dots$ at $m=6,8,10$ and $26,54,110,\dots$ at $m=7,9,11$.
\end{remark}

Two further facts about $h^{\ast}$ are cheap and worth recording, since
they bound what a construction may hope for. The first is that heights add
under a composition that keeps both walks going at once; the second is an
explicit operation, found in the course of the search for a family, that
raises the dimension by two and the height by a growing but merely
additive amount, and whose products leave the Holt--Klee class as soon as
they are interesting.

\begin{lemma}[Superadditivity of $h^{\ast}$]\label{lem:hstar-super}
$h^{\ast}(k+l)\ge h^{\ast}(k)+h^{\ast}(l)$ for all $k,l\ge1$. In particular
$h^{\ast}(6)\ge13$, $h^{\ast}(8)\ge14$ and $h^{\ast}(9)\ge19$, bounds that
\Cref{thm:blowup} below supersedes.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $s_1$ be an AUSO of the $k$-cube attaining $h^{\ast}(k)$, translated so
that its sink is $0$, and $s_2$ an AUSO of the $l$-cube attaining
$h^{\ast}(l)$; write $W(y):=y\oplus s_2(y)$ for the bottom-antipodal step of
$s_2$, and fix $z\in\{0,1\}^{l}$. On the $(k+l)$-cube, with vertices
$(v_1,v_2)$, put
\[
  s(v_1,v_2):=\bigl(s_1(v_1),\ s_2(v_2\oplus c(v_1))\bigr),\qquad
  c(v_1):=\begin{cases}z,&v_1\ne0,\\0,&v_1=0.\end{cases}
\]
This is a unique sink orientation: two vertices differing in the first
block are separated by $s_1$, and two with the same $v_1$ share $c(v_1)$
and are separated by the translate $y\mapsto s_2(y\oplus c)$ of $s_2$,
which is a USO because translation is an automorphism of the cube. It is
acyclic: an edge in a first-block coordinate is oriented by $s_1$ alone,
whatever $v_2$ is, so a directed cycle projects to a directed closed walk
of $s_1$, which is constant since $s_1$ is acyclic; the cycle then lies in
a single fibre $\{v_1\}\times\{0,1\}^{l}$, where the orientation is a
translate of the acyclic $s_2$.

Now follow the bottom-antipodal walk from $(v_1,v_2)$ with $v_1$ of
$s_1$-height $h^{\ast}(k)$. Both blocks step simultaneously. While
$v_1\ne0$ the second block satisfies $v_2\gets v_2\oplus s_2(v_2\oplus z)$,
that is, in the coordinates $y:=v_2\oplus z$ it performs the walk
$y\gets W(y)$ of $s_2$. After exactly $h^{\ast}(k)$ steps the first block
reaches $0$ and stays there, and from then on the second block performs
the walk of $s_2$ itself from the position $y\oplus z$ it has reached.
Choose $x^{\ast}$ of $s_2$-height $h^{\ast}(l)$, set
$z:=W^{h^{\ast}(k)}(x^{\ast})\oplus x^{\ast}$ and start the second block at
$v_2:=x^{\ast}\oplus z$, so that $y_0=x^{\ast}$: after the first phase the
second block sits at $y\oplus z=W^{h^{\ast}(k)}(x^{\ast})\oplus z=x^{\ast}$
and the second phase takes $h^{\ast}(l)$ further steps. The walk has length
$h^{\ast}(k)+h^{\ast}(l)$. The numerical cases use $h^{\ast}(5)=12$,
$h^{\ast}(4)=7$ and $h^{\ast}(1)=1$; we ran the construction on the
witnesses of \Cref{prop:auso-census} and \Cref{prop:hstar-five} and
confirmed, in each case, a unique sink orientation, acyclic, with a walk of
the stated length.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[A dimension-raising operation of quadratic height]\label{prop:D-quadratic}
For an AUSO $s_A$ of the $m$-cube with bottom-antipodal heights $h_A$, let
$D(s_A)$ be the outmap on the $(m+2)$-cube with vertices $(\alpha,\beta,v)$
whose inner part is $s_A(v\oplus\bar1)$ on the layer $(\alpha,\beta)=(0,0)$
and $s_A(v)$ on the other three layers, and whose outer part is
$\emptyset$ on $(0,0)$; $\{\alpha,\beta\}$ if $h_A(v)$ is even and
$\{\alpha\}$ if odd, on $(1,0)$; $\{\beta\}$ if even and $\{\alpha,\beta\}$
if odd, on $(0,1)$; and $\{\alpha\}$ if even and $\{\beta\}$ if odd, on
$(1,1)$. Starting from the $1$-cube, four iterations of $D$ give acyclic
unique sink orientations of the cubes of dimensions $3,5,7,9$ with
bottom-antipodal heights
\[
  4,\qquad 9,\qquad 16,\qquad 25,
\]
every bottom-antipodal trace of every start obeying \Cref{cor:no-return}
and \Cref{cor:law-b}. The orientation of dimension $3$ is Holt--Klee; those
of dimensions $5$ and $7$ are not, a $3$-face of each carrying only two
internally vertex-disjoint source-to-sink paths.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A computation, which we performed from the definition above and not from
the code of the search that produced it: for each of the four iterates we
checked the Szab\'o--Welzl condition $(s(u)\oplus s(v))\cap(u\oplus v)\ne
\emptyset$ on every pair, acyclicity by depth-first search, the two laws on
all $2^{m}$ traces, and the heights; the Holt--Klee test is a unit
vertex-capacity maximum flow on every face of dimension at least two,
validated on the twelve orientations of the $2$-cube, on the $656$
Holt--Klee orientations of the $3$-cube of \Cref{prop:auso-census}, and on
the non-Holt--Klee orientation of \Cref{prop:auso-seven}. The heights
$4,9,16,25$ are $(k+1)^{2}$ at the $k$-th iterate; whether that law
persists is not proved, and we do not use it.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:D-quadratic}
The operation was found by a search over rules of the Schurr--Szab\'o shape
--- outer outmap a function of the layer and of the inner height modulo a
small integer --- with the translation vector fixed at $\bar1$;
\Cref{thm:blowup} below shows that the vector was the only thing wrong with
it, since with $z=o\oplus u$ the same rule doubles. Its interest is the other
clause: from dimension $5$ on its products violate Holt--Klee, so by
\Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp} no nondegenerate one-player game realises them,
and any realisation --- which is the open question of \Cref{prop:auso-size}
for this family as for every other --- must use both players or ties
(\Cref{rem:oneplayer-lp}), as \Cref{prop:auso-seven} does with two players
at dimension $4$.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[A height-doubling blow-up]\label{thm:blowup}
Let $s$ be an acyclic unique sink orientation of the $m$-cube, $o$ its
sink, $u$ a vertex of maximal bottom-antipodal height $h:=h(u)$, and
$z:=o\oplus u$. On the $(m+2)$-cube with vertices $(\alpha,\beta,v)$ define
the outmap $B(s)$ by its inner part
\[
  I_{\alpha\beta}(v):=\begin{cases} s(v\oplus z),&(\alpha,\beta)=(0,0),\\
  s(v),&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}
\]
and its outer part $O_{\alpha\beta}(v)\subseteq\{\alpha,\beta\}$, which
depends only on the layer and on the parity of $h(v)$:
\[
 \begin{array}{c|cc}
   (\alpha,\beta) & h(v)\ \text{even} & h(v)\ \text{odd}\\ \hline
   (0,0) & \emptyset & \emptyset\\
   (1,0) & \{\alpha,\beta\} & \{\alpha\}\\
   (0,1) & \{\beta\} & \{\alpha,\beta\}\\
   (1,1) & \{\alpha\} & \{\beta\}
 \end{array}
\]
Then $B(s)$ is an acyclic unique sink orientation of the $(m+2)$-cube, and
its bottom-antipodal height is at least $2h+2$. Consequently
\[
  h^{\ast}(m+2)\;\ge\;2h^{\ast}(m)+2\qquad(m\ge1),
\]
so that $h^{\ast}(m)\ge3\cdot2^{(m-1)/2}-2$ for odd $m$ and
$h^{\ast}(m)\ge2^{m/2+1}-2$ for even $m$; seeded at $h^{\ast}(4)=7$ and
$h^{\ast}(5)=12$ instead, $h^{\ast}(m)\ge9\cdot2^{(m-4)/2}-2$ for even
$m\ge4$ and $h^{\ast}(m)\ge14\cdot2^{(m-5)/2}-2$ for odd $m\ge5$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $L$ for a layer, $O_L(v)$ for the outer part and $I_L(v)$ for the
inner part. Translation is an automorphism of the cube, so
$v\mapsto s(v\oplus z)$ is again an acyclic unique sink orientation of the
inner cube, with sink $o\oplus z=u$.

\emph{Unique sink.} Let $(L,v)\ne(L',v')$; we exhibit a coordinate in
$(B(s)(L,v)\oplus B(s)(L',v'))\cap((L,v)\oplus(L',v'))$. If $L=L'$ then
$v\ne v'$ and the inner parts are values of one unique sink orientation
($s$ or its translate), which supplies an inner coordinate. If $L\ne L'$
and $v=v'$, the required coordinate is outer, and it exists because for
each parity the outer rule is itself a unique sink orientation of the
$2$-cube: for even $h(v)$ the outmaps $\emptyset,\{\alpha,\beta\},
\{\beta\},\{\alpha\}$ at $(0,0),(1,0),(0,1),(1,1)$ satisfy the
Szab\'o--Welzl condition on all six pairs, and so do
$\emptyset,\{\alpha\},\{\alpha,\beta\},\{\beta\}$ for odd $h(v)$; the
inner parts, which may differ when one layer is $(0,0)$, are irrelevant
since no inner coordinate lies in $(L,v)\oplus(L',v')$. If $L\ne L'$ and
$v\ne v'$ with neither layer equal to $(0,0)$, both inner parts are values
of $s$ and supply an inner coordinate. Finally let $L=(0,0)\ne L'$ and
$v\ne v'$: then $O_L(v)=\emptyset$ while $O_{L'}(v')$ contains, in both
parities, a coordinate on which $L'$ has a $1$ --- $\alpha$ for
$L'=(1,0)$, $\beta$ for $L'=(0,1)$, and $\alpha$ or $\beta$ for
$L'=(1,1)$ --- and that coordinate lies in $L\oplus L'$.

\emph{Acyclic.} No outer edge leaves layer $(0,0)$, and inside it the
orientation is the translate of $s$, so a directed cycle meeting $(0,0)$
would lie in it and contradict acyclicity. A cycle avoiding $(0,0)$ uses
inner edges of the three layers $(1,0),(0,1),(1,1)$, all of which carry
the same $s$, and outer edges among them: $(1,0)\to(1,1)$ by $\beta$ at
even $h$, $(0,1)\to(1,1)$ by $\alpha$ at odd $h$, $(1,1)\to(0,1)$ by
$\alpha$ at even $h$ and $(1,1)\to(1,0)$ by $\beta$ at odd $h$. Projecting
the cycle to the inner cube gives a closed directed walk of $s$, hence a
constant $v$; but at a fixed $v$ the outer arcs among the three layers
form the directed path $(1,0)\to(1,1)\to(0,1)$ when $h(v)$ is even and
$(0,1)\to(1,1)\to(1,0)$ when it is odd --- $(1,0)$ and $(0,1)$ are not
adjacent in the cube --- and a directed path carries no cycle. So $B(s)$
has no directed cycle, and being a unique sink
orientation of a cube it has a unique sink, namely $(0,0,u)$.

\emph{Height.} Along the bottom-antipodal walk of $s$ the height drops by
exactly one per step. Start at $(1,0,u)$ if $h$ is even and at $(0,1,u)$
if $h$ is odd; in either case the outer part is $\{\alpha,\beta\}$ and the
inner part is $s(u)$, so one step leads to the other of the two layers
with the inner vertex advanced one step along the walk of $s$, whose
height now has the other parity --- and the outer part is again
$\{\alpha,\beta\}$. This repeats for $h$ steps, at the end of which the
inner vertex is $o$, of height $0$, and the layer is $(1,0)$ in both
cases. There $O=\{\alpha,\beta\}$ and $I=s(o)=\emptyset$, so the next
vertex is $(0,1,o)$, where $O=\{\beta\}$ leads to $(0,0,o)$: two more
steps. In layer $(0,0)$ the walk is, in the coordinates $y:=v\oplus z$,
the bottom-antipodal walk of $s$ from $y=o\oplus z=u$, which has $h$
further steps before reaching $y=o$, that is $v=u$, the sink of $B(s)$.
The walk has length $2h+2$. The numerical bounds follow by induction from
$h^{\ast}(1)=1$, $h^{\ast}(2)=2$, $h^{\ast}(4)=7$ and $h^{\ast}(5)=12$
(\Cref{prop:auso-census,prop:hstar-five}).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The height of a blow-up, exactly]\label{prop:blowup-height}
In the situation of \Cref{thm:blowup} write $h:=h_s$, $h_u:=h(u)$ and
$v':=v\oplus s(v)$ for the bottom-antipodal successor of an inner vertex.
The bottom-antipodal height $H$ of $B(s)$ is
\[
\begin{array}{ll}
 H(0,0,v)=h(v\oplus z), &
 H(1,1,v)=h(v)+2+h_u,\\[2pt]
 H(1,0,v)=\begin{cases} h(v)+2+h_u,& h(v)\ \text{even},\\
                        1+h(v'\oplus z),& h(v)\ \text{odd},\end{cases} &
 H(0,1,v)=\begin{cases} 1+h(v'\oplus z),& h(v)\ \text{even},\\
                        h(v)+2+h_u,& h(v)\ \text{odd}.\end{cases}
\end{array}
\]
In particular the height of $B(s)$ is exactly $2h_u+2$, attained exactly
at the vertices $(1,1,v)$ and, according to the parity of $h_u$, $(1,0,v)$
or $(0,1,v)$, over the vertices $v$ of maximal height --- twice their
number, which need not be a power of two ($32$ of the $728$ acyclic
unique sink orientations of the $3$-cube have six or seven maximal
vertices) --- and $h^{\ast}(m+2)\ge2h^{\ast}(m)+2$ is what the construction
gives, no more. The four formulas hold for $B(s,z)$ with an arbitrary $z$,
with $h(o\oplus z)$ in place of $h_u$; the height of $B(s,z)$ is then
$h_s+2+h(o\oplus z)$, equal to $2h_u+2$ exactly when $o\oplus z$ has
maximal height, and $h_s+2$ at $z=0$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
On the layer $(0,0)$ the outer part is empty, so the walk moves only the
inner coordinates, by $s(\cdot\oplus z)$: in the coordinates $y=v\oplus z$
it is the walk of $s$, of length $h(y)$. Elsewhere the step flips the
outer coordinates in $O_{\alpha\beta}(v)$ and the inner ones in $s(v)$ at
once, so the inner height drops by one per step. From $(1,0,v)$ with
$h(v)$ even the step lands at $(0,1,v')$ with $h(v')$ odd, from
$(0,1,\cdot)$ with odd height at $(1,0,\cdot)$ with even height, and so
on: after $h(v)$ steps the inner vertex is $o$ and the layer is $(1,0)$,
then $(1,0,o)\to(0,1,o)\to(0,0,o)$, and then $h(o\oplus z)=h_u$ further
steps. From $(0,1,v)$ with $h(v)$ odd and from $(1,1,v)$ in either
parity the walk enters that alternation after one step. From $(1,0,v)$
with $h(v)$ odd the outer part is $\{\alpha\}$ and the step lands in the
layer $(0,0)$ at $v'$, as it does from $(0,1,v)$ with $h(v)$ even, where
the outer part is $\{\beta\}$; the first clause then applies. The maximum
of the four expressions is $h_u+2+h_u$ (the round-$16$ level route;
verified here against the direct heights on six seeds, among them
$B^{1}$, $B^{2}$, $G^{\sharp}$'s orientation and the height-$11$ witness
of \Cref{prop:hkfive}, with no mismatch).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The layers are ordered]\label{lem:layer-order}
In $B(s)$, from $(1,0,v)$, $(0,1,v)$ and $(1,1,v)$ there are directed
paths along outer edges alone to $(0,0,v)$, for every inner $v$: by the
table of \Cref{thm:blowup}, $\alpha\in O_{1,0}(v)$ and
$\beta\in O_{0,1}(v)$ for both parities, and from $(1,1,v)$ the single
outer edge leads to $(0,1,v)$ or to $(1,0,v)$. Hence in every stopping
realisation of $B(s)$ the layer-$(0,0)$ value vector dominates the value
vector at every other layer with the same inner strategy, componentwise
(\Cref{lem:switch}): the translated layer is the top of its fibre.
\end{lemma}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:blowup-measured}
We ran the construction in exact arithmetic on the one-cube, on the
two-cube, on $G^{\sharp}$'s orientation and on the height-$12$
orientation of \Cref{prop:hstar-five}: writing the seed's own height
first, the heights are $1;4,10,22,46,94$; $2;6,14,30,62$; $7;16,34,70$;
and $12;26,54,110$, each iterate exactly $2h+2$, with the unique-sink and
acyclicity predicates confirmed at every step (the height-$12$ seed is
not printed here; on the printed height-$11$ witness of \Cref{prop:hkfive}
the iterates are $24,50,102$). Two
things the computation adds to the theorem. First, the translation vector
$z=o\oplus u$ is a \emph{single} coordinate at every level from the second
on --- one of the two coordinates introduced two dimensions earlier, namely
$\beta$ when the previous level's seed had odd height and $\alpha$ when it
had even height, hence $\alpha$ from the third level on, since every height
beyond the first level is even --- so the layer $(0,0)$ of a level presents
the previous level ``as if that coordinate had been flipped''. This is for
the vertex $u$ at which the theorem's walk starts; $B$ depends on the pair
$(s,u)$, and the other vertices of maximal height, of which there are
several at every level, give other translation vectors. Second, the vector is the whole difference
between doubling and not: with $z=\bar1$ the operation is $D$ of
\Cref{prop:D-quadratic} and the heights are $4,9,16,25,36$; with the
reversal $v\mapsto\bar1\oplus s(v)$ of all inner edges in place of the
translation --- the operation a game performs by exchanging its two sink
payoffs --- the heights are $4,10,16,23,30$, because the reversed
seed's walk from the old sink has length only $1,4,4,5,5$ at levels
$1,\dots,5$; and
reversing the inner edges in the directions of $z$ alone, $v\mapsto
s(v)\oplus z$, which is a unique sink orientation, is no longer acyclic
from the second level on.

The first level is realised: $B$ of the one-cube, the orientation
$(0,1,3,6,7,4,5,2)$ of height $4$, is the improvement orientation of a
nondegenerate one-player stopping SSG on $58$ vertices (three Max,
fifty-three average, two sinks), found by the integer search of
\Cref{prop:auso-seven}'s kind and verified from the game; it is Holt--Klee,
as \Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp} requires. The second level, of height $10$ at
dimension $5$, is not Holt--Klee, so any realisation needs Min vertices
or ties (\Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp}, \Cref{rem:oneplayer-lp}); it is
realised, with one Min vertex, in \Cref{prop:b2-realised} below. (Blind
searches for it, with two or three Min vertices, had not converged, and
had also failed to re-find the known walks of $G^{\sharp}$ and of a
one-player height-$6$ game within the same budget; the realisation was
found by design, not by search.)

A translated layer is realised at inner dimension two. The blow-up
depends on the pair $(s,u)$, and a height-$2$ orientation $s$ of the
$2$-cube has two vertices of maximal height: for every such $s$, the
choice with $z=o\oplus u$ a single coordinate gives one Holt--Klee class
of the $4$-cube of height $6$, with canonical outmap
$(0,1,3,2,7,6,4,13,15,14,12,9,11,10,8,5)$, and the choice with
$z=\bar1$ gives one class that is not Holt--Klee. The Holt--Klee class is
the improvement orientation of a nondegenerate one-player stopping SSG on
$100$ vertices (four Max, ninety-four average, two sinks), found by the
search of \Cref{rem:hk-survey} and verified from the game, with a
bottom-antipodal walk of length $6$. On its layer $(0,0)$ the inner
$2$-cube is translated by a \emph{single} coordinate $j$: the vertex $j$
has its preference reversed and the other inner vertex compares its
options as it would if $j$ were flipped --- exactly the operation that
\Cref{rem:blowup-realise} identifies as the obstacle, performed by the
monotone readouts of a one-player game. How the game does it has not been
analysed.

The theorem is also machine-checked. The file \texttt{lean/SSGProof/Blowup.lean}
of the repository formalises cube orientations as maps
$(\iota\to\mathbf{2})\to(\iota\to\mathbf{2})$, the Szab\'o--Welzl condition,
acyclicity as the existence of a bounded rank decreasing along every edge,
the bottom-antipodal step and the height function, and proves in Lean~4
--- with no import beyond the core library, and depending only on the
axioms \texttt{propext}, \texttt{Classical.choice} and \texttt{Quot.sound}
--- the three statements \texttt{isUSO\_blow} (for every $z$ and every
parity function), \texttt{isAcyclic\_blow} (likewise) and
\texttt{walk\_blow}: from the start of the proof the bottom-antipodal walk
of the blow-up reaches $(0,0,u)$ after exactly $2h(u)+2$ steps and at no
earlier step. Not formalised are the identification of $(0,0,u)$ as the
sink and the numerical corollaries. It is the first result of this
document to be formalised.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[The second level is realised, by two players]\label{prop:b2-realised}
There is a nondegenerate stopping SSG on $138$ vertices --- five Max, one
Min, $130$ average and the two sinks --- whose improvement outmap is
exactly the second level $B^{2}=B(B(s_1))$ of \Cref{thm:blowup} seeded at
the one-cube, namely
\[
  (7,4,5,2,0,1,3,6,24,9,27,14,31,28,29,10,16,25,19,30,23,20,21,26,8,17,11,22,15,12,13,18)
\]
in the coordinates (seed, $\alpha_1$, $\beta_1$, $\alpha_2$, $\beta_2$),
an acyclic unique sink orientation of the $5$-cube of bottom-antipodal
height $10$ that is not Holt--Klee; its all-switches run from $\sigma=12$
is $12,19,13,17,8,16,0,7,1,5,4$. Writing $c_1,\dots,c_5$ for the Max and
$c_6$ for the Min vertex, its harmonic normal form, as multiples of
$\tfrac1{512}$ over the targets $(c_1,\dots,c_6,t_1)$ with the remaining
mass going to $t_0$, is
\[
 \begin{array}{r@{\quad}l@{\qquad}l}
  c_1: & (12,461,36,0,0,0,0)     & (276,0,0,0,127,0,47)\\
  c_2: & (0,0,509,0,0,0,0)       & (0,83,0,0,320,0,12)\\
  c_3: & (0,0,0,0,0,460,43)      & (267,187,0,0,56,0,0)\\
  c_4: & (0,0,0,0,502,0,8)       & (71,293,23,0,0,0,0)\\
  c_5: & (0,0,0,509,0,0,0)       & (0,0,0,0,0,419,1)\\
