  c_6: & (472,0,0,0,0,0,0)       & (0,493,0,0,16,0,1).
 \end{array}
\]
Every row sum is below $512$, so every action leaks to $t_0$ and the game
is stopping.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The game was designed by the round-$15$ gadget route --- the inner three
Max vertices carry the first level, the outer pair the parity rule of
\Cref{thm:blowup}, and the Min vertex performs the layer-$(0,0)$
translation --- and its rows were fixed by exact linear programming over
the normal form. We verified it from the explicit game: the greatest trap
is empty (\Cref{lem:trapchar}); the $64$ profile values were computed by
exact elimination and $\val_\sigma$ taken as the minimum over the two Min
strategies; no incidence is tied; the outmap is the displayed one, a
unique sink orientation, acyclic, of height $10$, on which the
unit-vertex-capacity flow test fails; and along the run the value vector
increases at every step. The normal form above reproduces the outmap on
its own. A second realisation, at denominator $2^{10}$ on $155$ vertices,
is recorded in the repository.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[How the level-two game pins its outer pair]\label{rem:b2-anatomy}
Read off the printed normal form, the design of \Cref{prop:b2-realised}
is transparent at its outer pair $c_4=\alpha_2$, $c_5=\beta_2$: action
$0$ of $c_4$ reads only $c_5$, with $F_{c_4,0}=\tfrac{502}{512}y_{c_5}+\tfrac8{512}$,
action $0$ of $c_5$ reads only $c_4$, with $F_{c_5,0}=\tfrac{509}{512}y_{c_4}$,
and no inner row reads $c_4$. So at every one of the eight strategies of
the layer $(0,0)$ the two outer vertices form a closed two-cycle whose
values are the constants
\[
  \val(c_4)=\frac{8/512}{1-\frac{502\cdot509}{512^{2}}}=\frac{2048}{3313},\qquad
  \val(c_5)=\frac{509}{512}\cdot\frac{2048}{3313}=\frac{2036}{3313},
\]
independently of the inner values, and the inner block --- the rows of
$c_1,c_2,c_3$ and of the Min vertex $c_6$ with $y_{c_5}$ substituted ---
is a single frozen readout system driven by the scalar $t=y_{c_5}$: at
$t=0$ its outmap is $B^{1}$, and at $t=\tfrac{2036}{3313}$ it is the
translate $B^{1}(\cdot\oplus e_2)=(7,4,5,2,0,1,3,6)$, exactly. Off the
layer $(0,0)$ the drive $t$ ranges over $[0,0.3476\ldots]$, beyond the
window in which the frozen block still has outmap $B^{1}$ (measured: the
block has $B^{1}$ on a grid up to $0.186$ and the translate on a window
round $0.6145$ with fences $0.5802\ldots$ and $0.6222\ldots$; the exact
fences are in \Cref{rem:pinned-escape}), so the other
three layers realise $B^{1}$ through the feedback between $y_{c_5}$ and
the inner values and not as a frozen system. \Cref{lem:layer-order} says
why the pinned layer can sit above the others at all, and the one-player
game of \Cref{prop:xor} shows that pinning is not the only way to
translate a layer: there the outer values on the translated layer are
not constant. (All numbers exact, from the printed rows; the round-$16$
level route and its auditor.)
\end{remark}

The round-$15$ gadget route, which found the realisation above, also
proved what any realisation of the tower must look like locally. Four
statements survive its audits; all are constraints on the lower-bound
programme, not barriers in the sense of \Cref{sec:gap}, and cover none of
the mechanisms M1--M6 or \Cref{def:bsi}.

\begin{definition}[Reduced rows]\label{def:reduced-rows}
For a stopping $G$ in harmonic normal form, $v\in C$ and an action $a$
with row $(p^{v,a};q^{v,a})$ --- so that $p^{v,a}_v<1$, since otherwise
$v$ with its return paths would be a trap (\Cref{lem:trapchar}) --- put
$\hat p^{v,a}_u:=p^{v,a}_u/(1-p^{v,a}_v)$ for $u\ne v$ and
$\hat q^{v,a}:=q^{v,a}/(1-p^{v,a}_v)$, the first-passage law of the action
conditioned on leaving $v$ (Meyer's stochastic complement, from memory),
again substochastic; write $A_v(y):=\hat p^{v,0}\cdot y+\hat q^{v,0}$ and
$B_v(y):=\hat p^{v,1}\cdot y+\hat q^{v,1}$ on $\mathbb R^{C\setminus\{v\}}$.
With $y:=\val_\sigma|_{C\setminus\{x_i\}}$ one has
$\val_\sigma(x_i)=A_i(y)$ if $\sigma_i=0$ and $B_i(y)$ if $\sigma_i=1$, and
the margin $\Delta_i(\val_\sigma)$ of the incidence $(\sigma,x_i)$ is
$(1-p^{i,1-\sigma_i}_i)(B_i(y)-A_i(y))$ --- the division of
\Cref{lem:rise-bound}'s proof --- so the state of $x_i$ is visible in the
value vector only through the conjunction of $\sigma_i$ with its
incidence: $\val_\sigma(x_i)$ equals the reference $A_i(y)$ when
$\sigma_i=0$, exceeds it when $\sigma_i=1$ and $x_i$ is at rest, and
falls below it when $\sigma_i=1$ and $x_i$ is switchable.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Comparable rows cannot turn]\label{prop:rows-turn}
Let $G$ be nondegenerate stopping with improvement orientation $s_G$ and
$\preceq$ its reachability preorder, along which $\val$ is nondecreasing
(\Cref{lem:switch}). Write $g_i(\sigma)=+$ when the $i$-edge at $\sigma$
points from $\sigma_i=0$ to $\sigma_i=1$. If $\hat p^{i,1}\ge\hat p^{i,0}$
then $\{g_i=+\}$ is up-closed for $\preceq$, and down-closed if
$\hat p^{i,1}\le\hat p^{i,0}$; so if $\{g_i=+\}$ is neither, the two
reduced rows of $x_i$ are incomparable.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
$g_i(\sigma)$ is the sign of $B_i-A_i$ at $\val_\sigma$, an affine
functional with coefficients $\hat p^{i,1}-\hat p^{i,0}$, monotone under
the stated inequality whatever the constants.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Every vertex of a level-two realisation must turn]\label{cor:b2-rows}
In every nondegenerate stopping realisation of $B^{2}$ the reduced rows of
each of the five Max vertices are incomparable; in particular no Max
vertex has an action going straight to the sinks or returning only to
itself and the sinks. For $B^{1}$ only the coordinate $\beta_1$ needs
incomparable rows.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
A finite check on the outmaps (reproduced here): for each of the five
coordinates of $B^{2}$ there is a directed edge along which $g_c$ passes
from $+$ to $-$ and one along which it passes from $-$ to $+$, while for
$B^{1}$ the seed coordinate and $\alpha_1$ lack one of the two.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The crossing lemma]\label{lem:crossing}
Let $G$ be nondegenerate stopping, $F$ a $2$-face of the Max cube on the
coordinates $i\ne j$, and suppose one Min strategy $\tau$ is a best
response to all four strategies of $F$. Then (a) the four value vectors
lie in an affine plane; (b) in it the segment joining the two values
with $\sigma_i=0$ and the segment joining the two with $\sigma_i=1$ meet
iff $g_i$ is non-constant on $F$; (c) at most one of $g_i,g_j$ is
non-constant on $F$, and if $g_i$ is, no affine functional of the value
vector separates $\{\sigma_i=0\}$ from $\{\sigma_i=1\}$ on $F$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) $\val_\sigma$ is the unique common point of $|C|$ hyperplanes
$z_v=V^{\sigma_v}_v(z)$, $z_u=V^{\tau_u}_u(z)$, so the $|C|-2$ shared by
$F$ meet in a plane $\Pi$ of dimension exactly two containing the four
values (the principal submatrix of $I-P$ on $C\setminus\{i,j\}$ is
nonsingular, being transient in a stopping game). (b) With
$\varphi_a(z):=z_i-V^{a}_i(z)$ the values with $\sigma_i=a$ lie on
$\ell_a:=\{\varphi_a=0\}\cap\Pi$, and $\Delta_i=\varphi_0-\varphi_1$.
Neither $\varphi_a$ vanishes identically on $\Pi$, else both would vanish
at the two points of the other line, against nondegeneracy; so
$\ell_0,\ell_1$ are lines. If they were parallel, $\varphi_1=c\varphi_0+d$
on $\Pi$ and $\Delta_i=-d$ would be constant on $\ell_0$; so $g_i$
non-constant forces the lines to cross at $p$ with
$\varphi_0(p)=\varphi_1(p)=0$, and $\Delta_i$, affine and changing sign
between the two points of $\ell_0$, has its unique zero on $\ell_0$ at
$p$, strictly between them, likewise on $\ell_1$. Conversely if $g_i$ is
constant on $F$, $\Delta_i$ has one sign on the whole segment of $\ell_0$
and the segments are disjoint. (c) A square with both $g$'s non-constant
is either a directed $4$-cycle or has two sources and two sinks, in
neither case a unique sink orientation
(\Cref{prop:allsw-auso}); the last clause is (b) and the separation
theorem.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Where the Min vertex of a level-two realisation must act]\label{cor:b2-min}
Call a pair ($2$-face $F$, coordinate $c$) \emph{forbidden} for an
orientation when $F$ has exactly one non-constant coordinate $c'$
(\Cref{lem:crossing}(c) allows at most one) and $g_c$ on $F$ is exactly
the split by $\sigma_{c'}$. $B^{2}$ has exactly four forbidden pairs, all on the
face spanned by $\{x,\alpha_1\}$ at $\beta_1=0$: $\alpha_2$ at the layers
$(\alpha_2,\beta_2)=(0,1),(1,1)$ and $\beta_2$ at $(1,0),(1,1)$. Hence in
every nondegenerate stopping realisation of $B^{2}$ the Min best
response is non-constant on each of those three $2$-faces, and
$\Vmin\ne\emptyset$; $B^{1}$ has no forbidden pair. The forbidden-pair
test is a local necessary condition for one-player realisability: over
the $12640$ classes of the $4$-cube, none of the $6113$ Holt--Klee
classes has a forbidden pair, $4365$ of the $6527$ others have one and
$2162$ do not; the height-$7$ class has eight, on eight distinct
$2$-faces, each of which localises
\Cref{cor:seven-two-player} to a single $2$-face without Holt--Klee.
Along the tower the counts are $0,4,28,136,600$ at $k=1,\dots,5$. That
Holt--Klee implies the absence of forbidden pairs in general is observed
on that census and not proved.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
A forbidden pair is the configuration \Cref{lem:crossing}(c) excludes
when the Min response is constant on $F$; the counts are exhaustive
computations of the route, reproduced by its auditor, and the delivered
game of \Cref{prop:b2-realised} has a Min best response varying on each
of the three critical faces: its best response is unique at every Max
strategy, equal to the non-default action exactly at
$\sigma\in\{11,15,19,23,27,31\}$, and it varies on $32$ of the $80$
two-faces in all (recomputed here).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The parity is not readable from the Max rows]\label{cor:parity-unreadable}
In a nondegenerate one-player realisation of $B^{1}$, no affine
functional of the value vector has the sign of the parity of the
bottom-antipodal height.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
On the face $\beta_1=0$ the heights of $B^{1}$ are $0,1,2,3$ at
$(x,\alpha_1)=(0,0),(1,0),(0,1),(1,1)$, so the parity is the split by
$\sigma_x$, and $g_x$ is non-constant there, being the split by
$\sigma_{\alpha_1}$: \Cref{lem:crossing}(c), whose hypothesis holds since
with $\Vmin=\emptyset$ the empty strategy is a best response to all four.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:parity-b2}
The corresponding statement for $B^{2}$ fails, and fails on the only
layer where it could be asserted. On each of the three layers
$(\alpha_2,\beta_2)=(0,1),(1,0),(1,1)$ the coordinate $g_x$ is
non-constant on the face $\{x,\alpha_1\}$ at $\beta_1=0$, so
\Cref{lem:crossing}(c) would apply --- but those are exactly the faces of
\Cref{cor:b2-min}, on which no single Min strategy is a best response to
all four strategies, so its hypothesis is unsatisfiable in every
realisation. On the remaining layer $(\alpha_2,\beta_2)=(0,0)$, the
translated one, $g_x$ is constant on that face, and there a Max row
difference does read both parities: at
$(x,\alpha_1)=(0,0),(1,0),(0,1),(1,1)$ one has $g_{\alpha_1}=1,0,1,0$ and
$g_{\beta_1}=1,1,1,0$ against the parity $0,1,0,1$ of the inner $B^{1}$
height and the parity $0,0,0,1$ of the height of $B^{2}$ itself, so
$-\Delta_{\alpha_1}$ and $-\Delta_{\beta_1}$, differences of two entries
of the value vector, have the sign of the one and of the other (this
round's auditor). What survives is \Cref{cor:b2-min} alone.
\end{remark}

At the first level this is why a one-player outer pair cannot read the
inner parity through a Max row difference, which is an affine functional
of the value vector; a Min vertex escapes the lemma by clipping one value
and moving one of the four points off the plane --- the $\min(\theta,x)$
device of \Cref{rem:blowup-realise} --- and at the second level
\Cref{cor:b2-min} says where it has to act. How the one-player
game of \Cref{rem:blowup-measured} manages the first level is now
explicit.

\begin{proposition}[How the one-player game realises a translated layer]\label{prop:xor}
Let $H$ be the one-player game on $100$ vertices of
\Cref{rem:blowup-measured} realising $B$ of the $2$-cube, with normal form
over $(x_0,x_1,x_2,x_3\,|\,t_1)$ in $256$-ths
\[
 \begin{array}{r@{\quad}l@{\qquad}l}
  x_0: & (51,0,121,0\,|\,4) & (11,165,0,4\,|\,2)\\
  x_1: & (0,84,0,0\,|\,21) & (7,13,133,102\,|\,0)\\
  x_2: & (94,0,160,0\,|\,1) & (1,12,70,171\,|\,0)\\
  x_3: & (19,94,8,114\,|\,0) & (10,38,97,102\,|\,2),
 \end{array}
\]
$x_0,x_1$ the inner coordinates, $x_2=\beta$, $x_3=\alpha$, and let $L$
be the indicator of the layer $\sigma_2=\sigma_3=1$. Then, exactly:
(i) action $0$ of $x_1$ is a pure self-return, so
$\val_\sigma(x_1)=\tfrac{21}{172}$ whenever $\sigma_1=0$; (ii)
$256\Delta_1=7z_0-71z_1+133z_2+102z_3-21$ is positive exactly on the four
strategies with $L=1$, so $\val_\sigma(x_1)$ is a three-level signal,
equal to $\tfrac{21}{172}$ at $\sigma_1=0$, below it at $\sigma_1=1,L=0$
and above it at $\sigma_1=1,L=1$; (iii) $\Delta_0$ compares $z_1$ with
the threshold $T=(40z_0+121z_2-4z_3+2)/165$, and
$\max_{L=0}T<\tfrac{21}{172}<\min_{L=1}T$ over the strategies with
$\sigma_1=0$, and at $\sigma_1=1$ the same order between $z_1$ and $T$
holds strategy by strategy (all sixteen recomputed here), so $\Delta_0>0$
exactly when $\sigma_1\oplus L=0$: $x_0$ compares its options as it would at the
flipped $\sigma_1$ precisely on the layer. (Reproduced here.)
\end{proposition}

Two ingredients, then: the translated coordinate is pinned by a
self-returning action, so its value carries the layer in its two extreme
levels; and the other inner coordinate compares that signal against a
threshold into which the layer enters with the opposite sign, so a single
affine comparison realises the exclusive or. Neither survives to inner
dimension three unchanged: \Cref{cor:b2-rows} forbids the pinning at every
Max vertex of a level-two realisation, and there the translated
coordinate's incidence is $(x\wedge\alpha_1)\oplus L$, a conjunction as
well, which the two remaining inner coordinates must read.

\begin{theorem}[Alternation costs bits]\label{thm:alternation-bits}
Let $G$ be nondegenerate stopping, $\alpha\in\Vmax$, and
$\sigma_0,\dots,\sigma_h$ Max strategies with $\sigma_{t+1}=\sigma_t[S_t]$
for nonempty sets of strictly switchable vertices, along which the margin
of $\alpha$ alternates in sign. Then
$\sum_{t=1}^{h-1}|\Delta_\alpha(\val_{\sigma_t})|\le1$, so some margin is
at most $1/(h-1)$ and $|\Vavg|\ge\log_2(h-1)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
With $z_t:=\val_{\sigma_t}|_C$ and $d_t:=z_{t+1}-z_t\ge0$
(\Cref{lem:switch}), $\Delta_\alpha=w\cdot z+c$ with
$w=p^{\alpha,1}-p^{\alpha,0}$, $\|w\|_1\le2$; alternation gives
$|w\cdot d_t|=|\Delta_\alpha(z_{t+1})|+|\Delta_\alpha(z_t)|$, so
$2\sum_{t=1}^{h-1}|\Delta_\alpha(z_t)|\le\sum_t|w|\cdot d_t=|w|\cdot(z_h-z_0)\le2$.
The margins are differences of entries of $\val_\sigma$, multiples of
$1/D$ with $D\le2^{|\Vavg|}$ (\Cref{lem:denominator-sharp}), and nonzero.
\end{proof}

This is \Cref{thm:switch-count} localised to one alternating vertex ---
the same telescoping with the weight $w$ in place of the all-ones vector
--- and it gives a factor-two sharpening without the $N$: along the
bottom-antipodal walk of $B^{k}$ a fixed outer coordinate alternates
$\Theta(2^{k})$ times, so any realisation of $B^{k}$ needs
$|\Vavg|\ge k-O(1)$, which the tower's growth in dimension already
forces; nothing here bounds the denominator of the rows, which is the
quantity \Cref{rem:blowup-realise} asks about.


\begin{proposition}[A valid parity is affinely readable; the true one is not]\label{prop:parity-readable}
Let $Z=(\val_\sigma|_{C})_{\sigma}$ be the $32$ value vectors of
\Cref{prop:b2-realised}'s game, $W$ the bottom-antipodal walk of $B^{2}$ from
$12$ (eleven vertices $12,19,13,17,8,16,0,7,1,5,4$) and $h$ the height of
$B^{2}$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item no affine functional of the value vector has the sign of
      $h(v)\bmod2$ at all $32$ strategies --- the level-two analogue of
      \Cref{cor:parity-unreadable} (the route measured this by a
      floating-point linear programme; its auditor produced an exact
      Farkas certificate, so it is proved).
\item There is one whose sign is $h(v)\bmod2$ at the eleven strategies of $W$;
      over the functionals $y\mapsto(p^{1}-p^{0})\cdot y+(q^{1}-q^{0})$ with
      $(p^{a},q^{a})\ge0$ and $|p^{a}|_1+q^{a}\le1$ the largest attainable
      margin is $8.056\cdot10^{-4}$, and the induced total function
      $p=00010111011000001111101101110001$ (indexed by $v$) differs from
      $h\bmod2$ at the six strategies $6,10,20,21,22,26$, all off the walk.
\end{enumerate}
By \Cref{prop:blowup-height} this $p$ is admissible, so the outer pair of a
level-three realisation may read $p$ instead of the parity.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}[The outer half of the third level is realised]\label{prop:b3-outer}
There is a nondegenerate stopping SSG on $194$ vertices --- seven Max, one Min,
$184$ average and the two sinks --- whose improvement outmap is
$B_{p,0}(B^{2})$ for the $p$ of \Cref{prop:parity-readable}: an acyclic unique
sink orientation of the $7$-cube of bottom-antipodal height $12$ whose
all-switches run is
$44,83,45,81,40,80,32,71,33,69,36,68,4$, the first twelve steps of the walk of
$B_{p,8}(B^{2})$. Its harmonic normal form, as multiples of $\tfrac1{1024}$ over
the targets $(x,\alpha_1,\beta_1,\alpha_2,\beta_2,\alpha_3,\beta_3,c_{\min},t_1)$
with the remaining mass going to $t_0$, is the level-two normal form of
\Cref{prop:b2-realised} unchanged (its entries doubled) on
$x,\alpha_1,\beta_1,\alpha_2,\beta_2,c_{\min}$, together with
\[
 \begin{array}{r@{\quad}l@{\qquad}l}
  \alpha_3: & (0,0,0,0,0,0,1021,0\,|\,2) & (0,0,549,335,0,0,0,0\,|\,0)\\
  \beta_3:  & (0,0,0,0,0,1023,0,0\,|\,0) & (271,201,0,0,336,0,0,88\,|\,1).
 \end{array}
\]
The inner six vertices do not read $\alpha_3$ or $\beta_3$, so their values are
the level-two values in all four layers and the inner outmap is $B^{2}$ on each;
on the layer $(0,0)$ the outer pair is the closed two-cycle of
\Cref{rem:b2-anatomy} with $A=\tfrac{1021}{1024}$, $A'=\tfrac{1023}{1024}$,
$q=\tfrac1{512}$, $\Theta=\tfrac{2048}{4093}$. Verified from the game: the
greatest trap is empty, the $256$ profile values were computed by exact
elimination and $\val_\sigma$ taken as the minimum over the two Min strategies,
no incidence is tied, the outmap is the displayed orientation, a unique sink
orientation, acyclic, of height $12$, and the value vector increases at every
vertex along the run.
\end{proposition}

The round-$17$ realisation-space route wrote down the shape shared by
\Cref{prop:b3-outer} and by the outer pair of \Cref{prop:b2-realised}, proved
exactly when a game of that shape realises one blow-up level, and proved
that the shape cannot be iterated: pinned on top of pinned, it cannot double.
Like \Cref{prop:tstar} and \Cref{thm:deformed-flat} this closes a
construction avenue and is not a barrier in the sense of \Cref{sec:gap}; it
covers none of the mechanisms M1--M6 or \Cref{def:bsi}.

\begin{definition}[Driven blocks and the pinned shape]\label{def:pinned}
A \emph{driven block} $\mathfrak B$ is a harmonic normal form on Max
vertices $x_1,\dots,x_m$ and $k$ Min vertices whose rows may read, with
nonnegative weights, one external coordinate $\beta$ (the \emph{drive});
frozen at $y_\beta=t$ it is a stopping game with improvement outmap
$s_{\mathfrak B}(t)$ on the $m$-cube. Its \emph{pinned extension} by
$(A,A',q_A,q_B,R_\alpha,R_\beta)$ --- $A,A'\in(0,1)$, $q_A,q_B\ge0$,
$R_\alpha,R_\beta$ substochastic affine readouts of the block's values ---
is the game on $C\cup\{\alpha,\beta\}$ with $\alpha,\beta\in\Vmax$ and
\[
 \alpha:\ (0)\ Ay_\beta+q_A,\quad(1)\ R_\alpha;\qquad
 \beta:\ (0)\ A'y_\alpha+q_B,\quad(1)\ R_\beta,
\]
no row of $\mathfrak B$ reading $\alpha$. Put $\Theta_u:=(Aq_B+q_A)/(1-AA')$
and $\Theta_w:=A'\Theta_u+q_B$. The game of \Cref{prop:b3-outer} is the
pinned extension of the level-two game with $A=\tfrac{1021}{1024}$,
$A'=\tfrac{1023}{1024}$, $q_A=\tfrac1{512}$, $q_B=0$, and the level-two
game is itself a pinned extension of its inner block $\{c_1,c_2,c_3,c_6\}$
by the pair $(c_4,c_5)$ with $(A,A',q_A,q_B)=(\tfrac{502}{512},\tfrac{509}{512},\tfrac8{512},0)$
(\Cref{rem:b2-anatomy}); that inner block, whose only closed two-cycle
would have to lie among $c_1,c_2,c_3$ and does not, is not itself pinned.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[The level theorem for the pinned shape]\label{prop:pinned-level}
Let $G$ be a nondegenerate stopping pinned extension. On the layer
$(\sigma_\alpha,\sigma_\beta)=(0,0)$ one has $y_\alpha=\Theta_u$ and
$y_\beta=\Theta_w$ at every inner strategy $\sigma$, so the block is frozen
at the drive $\Theta_w$ there; on the other layers $y_\beta=A'R_\alpha+q_B$
at $(1,0)$ and $y_\beta=R_\beta$ at $(0,1),(1,1)$, read at that layer's
block values. Writing $a:=R_\alpha$, $b:=R_\beta$ at the layer's block
values, the improvement outmap of $G$ is $B_\varphi(s,z)$ of
\Cref{prop:blowup-readout} if and only if, for every inner $\sigma$,
\begin{align*}
 &\text{(C1)}\quad a<\Theta_u \ \text{ at } L\in\{(0,0),(1,0)\}; &
 &\text{(C2)}\quad b<\Theta_w \ \text{ at } L\in\{(0,0),(0,1)\};\\
 &\text{(C3)}\quad a-Ab-q_A>0\iff\varphi(\sigma)=1 \ \text{ at } L\in\{(0,1),(1,1)\}; &
 &\text{(C4)}\quad b-A'a-q_B>0\iff\varphi(\sigma)=0 \ \text{ at } L\in\{(1,0),(1,1)\};
\end{align*}
and (C5) the block's outmap at that layer's drive is $s(\sigma\oplus z)$ at
$\sigma$ on the layer $(0,0)$ and $s(\sigma)$ on the other three. With
$\Phi:=a-Ab-q_A$ and $\Psi:=b-A'a-q_B$, (C1) is $\Phi+A\Psi<0$ and (C2) is
$\Psi+A'\Phi<0$, so the outer part is the demand that one difference of two
substochastic readouts read $\varphi$ with a margin; and the drives off the
layer $(0,0)$ lie strictly below $\Theta_w$ (\Cref{lem:layer-order}, and
directly from (C1), (C2) at the layers $(1,0)$, $(0,1)$).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Freezing the outer pair at $(0,0)$ leaves $y_\alpha=Ay_\beta+q_A$,
$y_\beta=A'y_\alpha+q_B$, whose unique solution is $(\Theta_u,\Theta_w)$,
independent of the block since no block row reads $\alpha$; at $(1,0)$,
$y_\alpha=R_\alpha$ and $y_\beta=A'R_\alpha+q_B$; at $(0,1)$ and $(1,1)$,
$y_\beta=R_\beta$. Write $G_\alpha=1$ when action $1$ of $\alpha$ is the
better one at the current values, likewise $G_\beta$; $\alpha$ is switchable
iff $G_\alpha\ne\sigma_\alpha$. The table of \Cref{thm:blowup}, with
$\varphi$ in place of the parity, says layer by layer
$G_\alpha=\sigma_\beta\wedge[\varphi=1]$ and
$G_\beta=\sigma_\alpha\wedge[\varphi=0]$. Substituting the four layers'
values turns these eight demands into (C1)--(C4): at $(0,0)$ and $(1,0)$
the $\alpha$-comparison is $a\lessgtr\Theta_u$ in both cases, since
$a<A(A'a+q_B)+q_A$ iff $a(1-AA')<Aq_B+q_A$; at $(0,1)$ and $(1,1)$ it is
$a\lessgtr Ab+q_A$; dually for $\beta$. The inner coordinates give (C5),
the outmap of a block vertex being read at the block's values, which by
\Cref{lem:readout} are those of the block frozen at the layer's drive. The
last clauses: $\Phi+A\Psi=(1-AA')(a-\Theta_u)$ and
$\Psi+A'\Phi=(1-AA')(b-\Theta_w)$; and (C1) gives $A'a+q_B<\Theta_w$, (C2)
gives $b<\Theta_w$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[On the pinned layer an outer switchable set is up-closed]\label{lem:pinned-upclosed}
Let $H$ be a nondegenerate stopping pinned extension of a block
$\mathfrak B_0$ and, for $x\in\{\alpha,\beta\}$, let $E_x$ be the set of
inner strategies $\sigma$ at which $x$ is switchable on the layer $(0,0)$.
Then $E_x$ is up-closed for the componentwise order on the value vectors
of $\mathfrak B_0$ frozen at $\Theta_w$; in particular it is closed under
the improving switches, single or simultaneous, of $s_{\mathfrak B_0}(\Theta_w)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
On the layer $(0,0)$ the outer values are the constants $\Theta_u$,
$\Theta_w$ (\Cref{prop:pinned-level}), so $\alpha$ is switchable at
$(\sigma,(0,0))$ iff $R_\alpha(\val_\sigma)>\Theta_u$, and $\beta$ iff
$R_\beta(\val_\sigma)>\Theta_w$, with $\val_\sigma$ the block's value vector
at $\Theta_w$. A readout is $y\mapsto p\cdot y+r\Theta_w+q$ with
$p,r,q\ge0$, hence nondecreasing in $y$; and an improving switch of Max in
the stopping block raises $\val_\sigma$ componentwise (\Cref{lem:switch}).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[No pinned extension realises a translate by its own outer pair]\label{thm:pinned-no-translate}
Let $s$ be an acyclic unique sink orientation of the $m$-cube of height
$h\ge2$, let $\psi$ alternate along a bottom-antipodal walk of $s$ of
length $h$ (what \Cref{prop:blowup-readout}(b) asks of a readout), let
$z'\in\{0,1\}^{m}$, and let $\zeta\in\{0,1\}^{m+2}$ have a nonzero
component on $\alpha$ or on $\beta$. Then $B_\psi(s,z')(\cdot\oplus\zeta)$
is the improvement outmap of no nondegenerate stopping pinned extension
with outer pair $(\alpha,\beta)$ --- for any pin, any readouts and any block.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $\zeta=(w,\zeta_\alpha,\zeta_\beta)$ and let $T$ be the displayed
outmap. On the layer $(0,0)$ its inner part is that of $B_\psi(s,z')$ on
the layer $(\zeta_\alpha,\zeta_\beta)\ne(0,0)$ read at $v\oplus w$, namely
$s(\cdot\oplus w)$; by \Cref{prop:pinned-level}(C5) that is the block's
orientation at $\Theta_w$, whose bottom-antipodal walks are the
$w$-translates of those of $s$, along which $\psi(\cdot\oplus w)$
alternates. Its outer part on that layer is
$O_{(\zeta_\alpha,\zeta_\beta)}(v\oplus w)$ from the table of
\Cref{thm:blowup}: at $(1,0)$, $E_\alpha$ is everything and
$E_\beta=\{\psi(\cdot\oplus w)\ \text{even}\}$; at $(0,1)$,
$E_\alpha=\{\text{odd}\}$ and $E_\beta$ is everything; at $(1,1)$,
$E_\alpha=\{\text{even}\}$, $E_\beta=\{\text{odd}\}$. A walk of length
$h\ge2$ along which $\psi$ alternates contains a step from an even vertex
to an odd one and a step from an odd vertex to an even one; in each of the
three cases one of $E_\alpha,E_\beta$ is a nontrivial parity class and is
not closed under one of those steps, against \Cref{lem:pinned-upclosed}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Pinned on pinned does not reach $B^{3}$]\label{cor:pinned-no-doubling}
Call a game \emph{doubly pinned} when it is a pinned extension of a block
that is itself a nondegenerate stopping pinned extension whose two pin
rows read only each other and constants, so that they read neither the
new outer pair nor the drive --- the shape of \Cref{prop:b3-outer} itself,
whose inner pin rows read only $c_4$, $c_5$ and $t_1$. No doubly
pinned game has improvement outmap $B_\varphi(B^{2},z)$, for any readout
$\varphi$, when $z$ has a nonzero component on $(\alpha_2,\beta_2)$. Since
the height of $B_\varphi(B^{2},z)$ is at most $10+2+h_{B^{2}}(4\oplus z)$
for every readout $\varphi$ --- off the layer $(0,0)$ each
bottom-antipodal step lowers the inner height by one, at the inner sink
the outer square allows at most two further steps, and the layer $(0,0)$
contributes $h_{B^{2}}(4\oplus z)$; with the parity readout this is the
exact value of \Cref{prop:blowup-height} --- and the maximal vertices $12,14,28,30$ of
$B^{2}$ all differ from its sink $4$ in the $\alpha_2$ bit, every doubling
translation --- $z\in\{8,10,24,26\}$, and in particular the $\alpha_2$ of
$B^{3}=B(B^{2})$ (\Cref{rem:blowup-measured}) --- is excluded: the third
level of height $22$ is out of the shape's reach, the best height a doubly
pinned $7$-cube whose outmap is a blow-up $B_\varphi(B^{2},z)$ can have is
$10+2+\max\{h_{B^{2}}(4\oplus z):z\ \text{purely inner}\}=16$, and
\Cref{prop:b3-outer} realises $12$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Let the inner block $\mathfrak B$ be a pinned extension of $\mathfrak B_0$
with pin pair $(p,q)$, some pair of the five coordinates. Its pin rows
read only each other and constants, so at every drive the layer
$\{p=q=0\}$ of $\mathfrak B$ carries the constant outer values
$(\Theta_u,\Theta_w)$ of $\mathfrak B$'s own pin --- were the pin rows to
read the drive, those values would move with the inner strategy through
the drive at the upper layers, and the monotonicity argument would not
apply (the route's correctness auditor) --- and \Cref{lem:pinned-upclosed}
applies to $\mathfrak B$ at every drive: the
switchable sets of $p$ and $q$ on that layer are closed under the improving
switches of $\mathfrak B_0$ there. By \Cref{prop:pinned-level}(C5) for the
outer game, $\mathfrak B$ presents $B^{2}$ itself at the drives of the three
upper layers and $B^{2}(\cdot\oplus z)$ at $\Theta_w$. First, $(p,q)$ must
be $(\alpha_2,\beta_2)$: over the ten pairs of coordinates of $B^{2}$ and
the layer in which the pair rests, $(\alpha_2,\beta_2)$ is the only one at
which no switchable set of $p$ or $q$ contains a vertex that an improving
single switch of the other three coordinates leaves (exhaustive;
\texttt{scripts/round17-verify/rs\_upclosed.py}: $12,3,5,9,1,7,9,6,6$
violations for the other nine pairs). Second, with $(p,q)=(\alpha_2,\beta_2)$
the translate $B^{2}(\cdot\oplus z)$ presented at $\Theta_w$ is
$B_\psi(B^{1},e_{\beta_1})(\cdot\oplus z)$ with $\psi$ the parity of the
inner height, which alternates along the walk of $B^{1}$ of length $4$, so
\Cref{thm:pinned-no-translate} forbids every $z$ with a nonzero component
on $(\alpha_2,\beta_2)$ (the same exhaustive check finds $3,3,5,5$
violations at $(\alpha_2,\beta_2)$ for $z=8,10,24,26$). The cube arithmetic
is as stated: over purely inner $z$ the largest $h_{B^{2}}(4\oplus z)$ is $4$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Where the obstruction sits, and what it leaves open]\label{rem:pinned-escape}
The obstruction is at the outer pair, not at the block, and it is
shape-specific. Two published facts sit outside the shape: the level-two
game itself is singly pinned, its inner block having no pin pair
(\Cref{def:pinned}), and the one-player game of \Cref{prop:xor}
translates a layer with non-constant outer values (\Cref{rem:b2-anatomy});
so the doubling that \emph{is} realised, from level one to level two, is
not one the corollary speaks about. The escape it names is explicit: let
the rest action of an outer vertex read the block as well as its partner,
so that on the layer $(0,0)$ its value is a monotone function of the
block's values rather than a constant and the switchability condition
becomes a difference of two monotone functionals; \Cref{lem:pinned-upclosed}
then says nothing. Whether a driven block that is not pinned can present
$B^{2}$ at one drive and $B^{2}(\cdot\oplus z)$ at another for one of the
four doubling translations $z\in\{8,10,24,26\}$ is the question every
route to the height-$22$ level now runs through; nothing here touches it.
Two exact data from the same route: the level-two block
$\{c_1,c_2,c_3,c_6\}$ driven by $y_{c_5}$ has outmap $B^{1}$ exactly on
$[0,\tfrac{47723619}{255897160})$ and $B^{1}(\cdot\oplus e_{\beta_1})$
exactly on $(\tfrac{1175936917}{2026652880},\tfrac{10168377}{16341335})$,
the fences that \Cref{rem:b2-anatomy} measured on a grid (the route's
auditor confirmed them consistent); and $B^{2}$ is realised by a game on
$137$ vertices, one fewer than \Cref{prop:b2-realised}, with the same
block and readouts and the pin $(A,A',q_A)=(\tfrac{500}{512},\tfrac{511}{512},\tfrac8{512})$,
verified here from the game (\texttt{scripts/round17-verify/B2\_pin137\_GAME.json}).
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[What a game would have to read]\label{rem:blowup-realise}
\Cref{thm:blowup} sharpens the realisability question of
\Cref{prop:auso-size} to a statement about what the values of a game can
express. Two of its three ingredients are available. Its outer
coordinates switch in every round of the inner walk, and a switching
vertex of a game can do that: on the ladder $L_n$ a Max vertex whose two
options are mixtures of the values of two vertices of $L_n$ with a suitable
threshold flips up to $3,4,4,4,7,8,8,8$ times along the run of length
$n=3,\dots,10$ --- the maximum over all pairs of vertices and all
thresholds, in exact arithmetic, the comparison being between two rows so
that the two values enter with opposite signs; a vertex with one constant
option flips at most twice (\Cref{lem:switch}); for the pair $(v_1,v_2)$
itself the counts are $3,4,3,4,5,8,3,4$. And the inner walk is run twice, which
in a game means that the inner values keep increasing, as \Cref{lem:switch}
guarantees along every run. The third ingredient needs care, and a first analysis of it was wrong.
The condition is not the obstacle: since the outer rule returns
$\emptyset$ exactly on the layer $(0,0)$, the orientation $B(s)$ is
unchanged if one reads its definition as ``translate the inner orientation
exactly when both outer coordinates are at rest'', and rest is readable
with one round of lag --- a Max vertex $x$ with successors $p,q$ is at rest
iff $\val(x)=\max(\val(p),\val(q))$, and the right-hand side is the value
that a fresh Max vertex over $p$ and $q$ holds once it is itself at rest
(\Cref{rem:closed-now-or-never}, \Cref{lem:max-tree}). The obstacle is the operation. On that layer
every inner vertex must compare its options as it would at $v\oplus e_j$,
and the state of $j$ reaches the rest of the game only through
$\val(j)$, so the inner game would have to be shown the \emph{anti-value}
$\val(j^{(0)})+\val(j^{(1)})-\val(j)$ of $j$, which is strictly decreasing
in $\val(j)$. No vertex of any SSG has such a value: under fixed
strategies every value is a nonnegative combination of the others, and
Min's best response is a minimum of such combinations, so every value is nondecreasing in every other. Substituting a value therefore
cannot do it, and the realised blow-up of the $2$-cube in
\Cref{rem:blowup-measured} shows that a game does it by other means at
inner dimension two. What a gadget could still do is reproduce the \emph{signs}: an inner
vertex whose two options read $j$ with weights $w_p,w_q$ compares, at
$v\oplus e_j$, a quantity in which the coefficient of $\val(j)$ has the
opposite sign and the constant is shifted by
$(w_p-w_q)(\val(j^{(0)})+\val(j^{(1)}))$; both the swapped weights and the
shift are mixtures of readable values, and the conditional presence of a
summand is available through a Min vertex against a threshold signal
$\theta$, since $\min(\theta,x)$ is $x$ when $\theta$ is high and $\theta$
when it is low. Whether those pieces assemble into a game --- with the
threshold signal itself produced from the two rest predicates, and with
the precision that the nested scales require --- is a finite design
problem that we have not carried out. The substitutes we did try ---
exchanging the sink payoffs, thresholds on monotone quantities, the
switchability of the previous level's outer vertices --- give heights
$4,10,16,23,30$, at most $4,6,8$ and at most $4,8,10$ respectively in the
same rule family, that is linear growth. \Cref{prop:b2-realised} shows the gadget exists at the second level, at
a cost of two Max vertices, one Min vertex and three further bits of
precision over the first. So the question is whether every level can be
added at \emph{additive} cost --- $O(1)$ controlled vertices and $O(1)$
bits of precision per level, so that level $k$ has $O(k)$ controlled
vertices and dyadic probabilities of denominator $2^{O(k)}$, hence
polynomially many average vertices: if so, an SSG-realisable family of
exponential bottom-antipodal height on polynomially many vertices exists
and the pivot of \Cref{rem:auso-size} is settled, whereas a cost of
$N\mapsto N^{c}$ per level would not do, by \Cref{prop:auso-size}. The
third level, dimension $7$ and height $22$, is the smallest open instance.
Nothing here proves or refutes the additive regime beyond one step, and
nothing follows from a failure about other constructions; note that
\Cref{lem:hstar-super}'s composition is itself a conditional translation
--- $c(v_1)=z$ off the first block's sink is what makes the two heights
add rather than maximise --- and that it grows only linearly. A datum on the negative side: the linear-programming orientation of the Klee--Minty cube, on which
Dantzig's rule is exponential, has bottom-antipodal height exactly $d$ in
every dimension $d\le12$, exact arithmetic again. Two oracles, finally,
separated by nothing here: an algorithm that reads only the orientation
(the strictly switchable set at each queried strategy) against one that
reads the values. At $m=3$, where every acyclic unique sink orientation is
realised (\Cref{prop:m3-realised,prop:m3-one-min}), naming the sink of an
unknown one of the $728$ from out-set queries alone costs exactly $4$ in
the worst case (exact minimax, \texttt{scripts/round17-verify/dout3.py};
$3$ once the algorithm is told which realisation's skeleton it faces, the
route's correctness auditor), while by \Cref{thm:eval-queries} $|C|+2\ge5$
value queries always suffice, so on that data the orientation oracle is the
cheaper of the two (the round-$18$ paper audit, correcting a claim of the
opposite here); whether
the outmap-query complexity of the SSG-realisable orientations is
superlinear is open (for arbitrary unique sink orientations a
superlinear deterministic bound is Schurr and Szab\'o's, from memory,
unchecked), and it is the information-theoretic form of the question
this remark asks.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[One-player realisability at $m=4$, measured]\label{rem:hk-survey}
How far \Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp} is from a characterisation was measured
on the $4$-cube, whose $12\,640$ classes of acyclic unique sink
orientations under the $384$ cube automorphisms have bottom-antipodal
heights $1,2,3,4,5,6,7$ with multiplicities
$1,754,4776,5404,1561,143,1$, of which $56$ classes of height $6$ and
none of height $7$ are Holt--Klee. A search over harmonic normal forms
--- an evolution strategy over the average part, an exact linear
programme over the controlled rows, then dyadic rounding and an exact
check of the resulting outmap --- realised \emph{every one} of the $56$
Holt--Klee classes of height $6$ by a nondegenerate one-player stopping
SSG, $53$ of them exactly at denominator $1024$, while $33$ random
non-Holt--Klee classes of height $6$ all failed, as
\Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp} demands; of $60$ random Holt--Klee classes of
height $5$ and $60$ of heights $3$ and $4$, all but five were realised
within the same budget. At $m=4$, then, Holt--Klee appears to be the
whole condition for one-player realisability; this is a measurement, and
the five unresolved classes are the place to look for a further law.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Ties in one-player games}\label{sec:ties}

\Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp} bounds the improvement orientation of a
\emph{nondegenerate} one-player stopping game by Holt--Klee, and
\Cref{rem:oneplayer-lp} records that the hypothesis is genuine: a flat
resolution (\Cref{thm:flat-resolution}) is a combinatorial completion,
not an LP orientation, so the ceiling $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}$ was not
known to survive degeneracy. This subsection closes that gap at $m=4$,
the one size at which $h^{\ast}$ and $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}$ differ and
a census exists, and determines exactly how long a tie at the value $0$
can be made to last. The results are the round-15 degenerate route's,
re-verified here.

The proof of \Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp} uses nondegeneracy only in its
last paragraph. Without it, one still has: the vertices of $X(G)$ are
the $2^{m}$ points $y^{\sigma}\ge\bbone$, $X(G)$ is a simple
$m$-polytope whose graph is the $m$-cube and whose faces are its
subcubes, $\mathrm{obj}(y^{\sigma})=\sum_v\val_\sigma(v)$, and for every
$\sigma$ and $v\in\Vmax$ the difference
$\mathrm{obj}(y^{\sigma[v]})-\mathrm{obj}(y^{\sigma})$ is positive, zero
or negative according as $v\in S_\sigma$, $v\in D_\sigma$ (the tied
directions of \Cref{def:flat}) or neither --- by \Cref{lem:switch},
\Cref{lem:trichotomy}(c) and \Cref{lem:trichotomy}(b) at $\sigma[v]$
respectively, summed over the vertices. So the flat classes are exactly
the maximal faces of $X(G)$ on which $\mathrm{obj}$ is constant, and
$(S,D)$ is the partial orientation $\mathrm{obj}$ induces on the graph of
$X(G)$ (bounded, by the recession argument in the proof of
\Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp}).

\begin{lemma}[Generic tie-breaking]\label{lem:tie-perturb}
Let $G$ be a stopping SSG with $\Vmin=\emptyset$, $|\Vmax|=m$, and
all-switches run $\sigma_0,\dots,\sigma_L$. Let $d$ be a linear
functional on $\mathbb R^{2m}$ and $d'$ a linear functional
non-constant on every edge of $X(G)$ on which both $\mathrm{obj}$ and $d$
are constant. For all sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$ the functional
$c:=\mathrm{obj}+\varepsilon d+\varepsilon^{2}d'$ is non-constant on every
edge of $X(G)$, and the orientation $s_\varepsilon$ of the $m$-cube it
induces is an acyclic unique sink orientation satisfying Holt--Klee on
every face, with $S(\sigma)\subseteq s_\varepsilon(\sigma)\subseteq
S(\sigma)\cup D(\sigma)$ for every $\sigma$. If moreover
\[
  (\star)\qquad d(y^{\sigma_t})>d\bigl(y^{\sigma_t[v]}\bigr)
  \qquad\text{for every }t\le L\text{ and every }v\in D(\sigma_t),
\]
then $s_\varepsilon(\sigma_t)=S(\sigma_t)$ for every $t$, the run is the
bottom-antipodal walk of $s_\varepsilon$ from $\sigma_0$, and
$L\le h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(m)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
$X(G)$ has finitely many edges. Let $\delta>0$ be the least nonzero
$|\Delta\mathrm{obj}|$ over them. For $\varepsilon$ small enough that
$\varepsilon\max|\Delta d|+\varepsilon^{2}\max|\Delta d'|<\delta$ and
$\varepsilon\min\{|\Delta d|:\Delta d\ne0\}>\varepsilon^{2}\max|\Delta d'|$,
the sign of $\Delta c$ on an edge is the sign of $\Delta\mathrm{obj}$ if
that is nonzero, else of $\Delta d$ if that is nonzero, else of
$\Delta d'$, which is nonzero by hypothesis; so $c$ is a generic linear
functional on $X(G)$ --- the lexicographic perturbation of a degenerate
objective, a textbook device (from memory: Charnes; Dantzig, Orden and
Wolfe). The orientation induced on the graph of a polytope by a generic
linear functional has a unique sink on every face, the face's unique
maximiser, is acyclic, and satisfies Holt--Klee on every face by Holt and
Klee's theorem; the faces of $X(G)$ are the subcubes. The inclusion is
the sign law above. Under $(\star)$ no flat direction is out at a run
vertex, so $s_\varepsilon(\sigma_t)=S(\sigma_t)$ and the
bottom-antipodal walk of $s_\varepsilon$ from $\sigma_0$ is the run.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[One tied vertex on the run is free]\label{cor:one-tie}
If all the flat incidences on the run occur at a single Max vertex $x$
and all with the same action $a$ --- in particular if the run has at
most one flat incidence --- then $(\star)$ holds with $d(y):=y_{x,a}$,
and $L\le h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(m)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For a flat incidence $(\sigma_t,x)$ with $\sigma_t(x)=a$,
$d(y^{\sigma_t})=y^{\sigma_t}_{x}\ge1$ while $y^{\sigma_t[x]}$ vanishes
on the coordinate $(x,a)$, so $d(y^{\sigma_t[x]})=0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The height-seven orbit admits one tie]\label{lem:seven-flat}
Let $s$ be one of the $384$ outmaps of bottom-antipodal height $7$ of
the $4$-cube and $\sigma_0$ a start whose walk has length $7$; there are
$768$ such pairs. Consider partitions of the $4$-cube into subcubes with
(F1) $s(\sigma)\oplus s(\sigma')=\sigma\oplus\sigma'$ inside a block;
(F2) $s(\sigma_t)\cap D_{A(\sigma_t)}=\emptyset$ at every walk vertex,
$D_A$ the direction set of the block $A$; (F3) the quotient digraph on
blocks, with arcs from the non-flat $s$-edges, acyclic; and (F4) for
every face $F$ with free set $J$, the set
$\{\sigma\in F:(s(\sigma)\setminus D_{A(\sigma)})\cap J=\emptyset\}$ equal
to $A\cap F$ for a single block $A$. Exactly $384$ of the pairs, one for
each $s$, admit a partition with a block of dimension at least $1$; in
each such case the partition is unique, and it consists of the single
edge $\{\sigma_0,\sigma_0\oplus e_x\}$ at the \emph{start}, all other
blocks being singletons.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
A finite exhaustive computation over the orbit, which is free and is
generated from $s=(0,1,3,6,7,4,13,10,14,15,9,12,11,8,5,2)$ by the $384$
automorphisms $v\mapsto\pi(v)\oplus t$. For each $s$ the subcubes
satisfying (F1) are exactly four edges, pairwise disjoint, so there are
$16$ candidate partitions, each tested against (F2)--(F4). For the
representative, whose walks of length $7$ start at $8$ and at $9$, the
(F1)-edges are $\{0,1\},\{6,14\},\{7,15\},\{8,9\}$: the first has both
ends on the walk $8,6,11,7,13,5,1,0$, which (F2) forbids; the second and
third violate (F2) because $3\in s(6)$ and $3\in s(7)$; the fourth
survives from the start $8$, where $0\notin s(8)$, and fails from the
start $9$, where $0\in s(9)$. (We recomputed this from the orientation.)
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[One player cannot reach seven, with or without ties]\label{thm:no-seven}
No stopping SSG with $\Vmin=\emptyset$ and $|\Vmax|=4$ has an
all-switches run of length $7$, degenerate or not. Hence the conclusion
of \Cref{cor:seven-two-player} about \emph{runs} needs no nondegeneracy
hypothesis: the two Min vertices of $G^{\sharp}$ are necessary for any
game whose run realises the height-seven walk. (A degenerate game whose
corner resolution lies in the height-seven orbit while all its runs are
shorter is not excluded, and is not what the corollary is about.)
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Suppose $G$ is such a game, with run $\sigma_0,\dots,\sigma_7$. The run
vertices lie in pairwise distinct flat classes (\Cref{lem:flat-class}),
so $c_{A(\sigma_t)}:=\sigma_t$ is a consistent choice of corners; extend
it arbitrarily and let $s'$ be the resolution of
\Cref{thm:flat-resolution}. By \Cref{cor:ceiling-general} the run is the
bottom-antipodal walk of $s'$ from $\sigma_0$, so $s'$ has height
$7=h^{\ast}(4)$ and lies in the single free orbit of
\Cref{prop:auso-census}. The flat partition of $G$ satisfies (F1)--(F4)
of \Cref{lem:seven-flat} with respect to $s'$ and $\sigma_0$: (F1)
because $s'(\sigma)=S_A\cup(\sigma\oplus c_A)$ on a class $A$; (F2)
because $s'(\sigma_t)=S_{A(\sigma_t)}$ is disjoint from
$D_{A(\sigma_t)}$; (F3) by \Cref{lem:flat-class}; (F4) by
\Cref{lem:face-sink}. So either $G$ is nondegenerate, or its only
nontrivial flat class is the edge $\{\sigma_0,\sigma_0\oplus e_x\}$ and
the run carries exactly one flat incidence. In both cases
\Cref{cor:one-tie} and \Cref{lem:tie-perturb} give a generic linear
functional on $X(G)$ whose orientation agrees with $S$ off that edge and
equals $s'$ on it; so $s'$ is an LP orientation and satisfies
Holt--Klee, which no outmap of height $7$ on the $4$-cube does
(\Cref{prop:auso-census}, $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(4)=6$).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The one-player ceiling up to $m=4$]\label{cor:hstar-one}
Write $h^{\ast}_{1}(m)$ for the greatest length of an all-switches run
of a stopping SSG with $\Vmin=\emptyset$ and $|\Vmax|=m$, ties allowed.
Then
\[
  h^{\ast}_{1}(m)=h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(m)=1,\;2,\;4,\;6\qquad(m=1,2,3,4),
\]
against $h^{\ast}(m)=1,2,4,7$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Upper bounds: for $m\le3$, $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(m)=h^{\ast}(m)$ and
\Cref{cor:ceiling-general} applies; $m=4$ is \Cref{thm:no-seven}. Lower
bounds: the ladders $L_1,L_2$ (\Cref{def:ladder}; all-switches takes
exactly $1$ and $2$ rounds on them, \Cref{rem:ladder}, and both are
nondegenerate), the $58$-vertex game of \Cref{rem:blowup-measured} (length
$4$) and the height-$6$ games of \Cref{rem:hk-survey} (length $6$), all
nondegenerate.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[$h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(5)=11$]\label{prop:hkfive}
No Holt--Klee acyclic unique sink orientation of the $5$-cube has
bottom-antipodal height $12$, and one has height $11$: the outmap
\[
  (7,14,28,13,24,25,26,27,15,16,17,22,19,4,5,18,30,29,0,31,3,10,12,9,23,2,8,1,11,20,21,6),
\]
whose walk from $0$ is $0,7,28,23,30,11,29,9,25,27,26,18$. Hence
$h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(m)=1,2,4,6,11$ for $m\le5$, against
$h^{\ast}(m)=1,2,4,7,12$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By \Cref{lem:auso-laws} a walk of length $12$ is one of the $347640$
law-abiding sequences of \Cref{prop:hstar-five} from $\sigma_0=0$.
Completing each of them over the remaining $19$ vertices under the
Szab\'o--Welzl condition and keeping the acyclic completions gives exactly
$480$ orientations (the program of \Cref{prop:hstar-five}, modified to
print every completion rather than stop at the first), and the
unit-vertex-capacity maximum-flow test of \Cref{cor:seven-two-player}
finds none of them Holt--Klee on every face. The displayed orientation is
a unique sink orientation, acyclic, of height $11$ along the stated walk,
and Holt--Klee on all its faces. Both computations were made here; the
value $11$ had been reported by two earlier searches.
\end{proof}

The witness carries up: the product of \Cref{lem:hstar-super} with the
witness as its first block, the $1$-cube as its second and $z=1$ is
Holt--Klee of height $12$, and once more of height $13$, so
$h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(6)\ge12$ and $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(7)\ge13$
(verified here); the product with the blocks in the other order is not
Holt--Klee, and \Cref{rem:four-ceilings} records what is known of the
growth of $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}$, now the operative unknown.

The same computation applies to the one height-$12$ orientation of the
$5$-cube we can name: for $s_{12}$ of \Cref{prop:hstar-five}, whose
walks of length $12$ start at $0$ and $16$, the only partition
satisfying (F1)--(F4) with a nontrivial block is the edge $\{0,16\}$ at
the start $0$, so no one-player stopping game with $|\Vmax|=5$ has an
all-switches run \emph{of length $12$} whose resolution with corners on
the run lies in the orbit of $s_{12}$. This does not give $h^{\ast}_{1}(5)\le11$: other
height-$12$ classes of the $5$-cube exist and cannot be enumerated
(\Cref{prop:auso-size}); $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(5)=11$ is established
in \Cref{prop:hkfive} above. What the two computations show is how constrained the
mechanism is by which a tie could beat the Holt--Klee ceiling: in both
extremal orientations the only tie a maximal run may carry sits on one
edge at the start, where a single coordinate functional breaks it.

\begin{lemma}[Where a tie on a run can sit]\label{lem:tie-partner}
Let $G$ be stopping with $|\Vmax|=m$, let $\sigma_0,\dots,\sigma_L$ be an
all-switches run with switched sets $S_t$, and let $s'$ be the resolution
of \Cref{thm:flat-resolution} with corners on the run, so
$s'(\sigma_t)=S_t$. If the incidence $(\sigma_t,v)$ is tied then
$v\notin S_t$, $\val_{\sigma_t[v]}=\val_{\sigma_t}$, $S(\sigma_t[v])=S_t$,
$\sigma_t[v]$ is not on the run, and $s'(\sigma_t[v])=S_t\cup\{v\}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
A tie is not a strict improvement, and the equality of the value vectors
is \Cref{lem:trichotomy}; at $u\ne v$ the margin depends only on the value
vector and $\sigma(u)$, and at $v$ both incidences are tied; the
strategies of a run carry pairwise distinct value vectors
(\Cref{lem:flat-class}); and the flat class of $\sigma_t$ is a subcube on
which $S$ and the tied set $D$ are constant with corner $\sigma_t$, so
\Cref{thm:flat-resolution} gives $s'(\sigma_t[v])=S_t\cup\{v\}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Two ties are separable unless they sit at one vertex with opposite actions]\label{lem:two-ties}
Let $G$ be one-player stopping, $y^{\sigma}$ the vertices of its occupancy
polytope $X(G)$, $D_{t,v}:=y^{\sigma_t}-y^{\sigma_t[v]}$, and let
$(t_1,v_1)\ne(t_2,v_2)$ be tied incidences of its run. Then
$0\in\operatorname{conv}\{D_{t_1,v_1},D_{t_2,v_2}\}$ only if $v_1=v_2$ and
$\sigma_{t_1}(v_1)\ne\sigma_{t_2}(v_1)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
$y^{\sigma}$ is supported on $\{(u,\sigma(u))\}$ with entries at least $1$
(\Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp}). Put $a:=\sigma_{t_1}(v_1)$. If $v_2=v_1=:v$
with $\sigma_{t_2}(v)=a$, then both $D_{t_1,v}$ and $D_{t_2,v}$ have the
entry $y^{\sigma}_{v,a}\ge1$ at the coordinate $(v,a)$, so every convex
combination of them is positive there and none vanishes. If $v_2\ne v_1$,
then $\sigma_{t_2}$ and
$\sigma_{t_2}[v_2]$ take the same action at $v_1$: if it is $1-a$, read
the coordinate $(v_1,a)$, where $D_{t_1,v_1}$ has an entry $\ge1$ and
$D_{t_2,v_2}$ vanishes; if it is $a$, read $(v_1,1-a)$, where
$D_{t_1,v_1}$ has an entry $\le-1$ and $D_{t_2,v_2}$ vanishes. Either way
a vanishing convex combination has first coefficient $0$, so
$D_{t_2,v_2}=0$, which is false since the two supports differ.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The walk of the second level needs the Min vertex]\label{thm:b2-walk}
No stopping SSG with $\Vmin=\emptyset$, degenerate or not, has as its
all-switches run the bottom-antipodal walk
$13,18,12,16,9,17,1,6,0,4,5$ of $B^{2}$ in the representative of
\Cref{prop:b2-realised} translated by $1$ --- exchanging the two listed
successors of the first Max vertex is a game isomorphism carrying
$\sigma$ to $\sigma\oplus1$, so this is that proposition's run
$12,19,13,17,8,16,0,7,1,5,4$. So the one Min vertex of that
realisation is necessary for its walk, not only for its orientation.
This is out of reach of the height argument, the walk having length
$10\le11=h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(5)$, and it rests, through
\Cref{lem:tie-perturb}, on Holt and Klee's theorem. It is narrower than
\Cref{thm:no-seven}: $B^{2}$ has four bottom-antipodal walks of length
$10$ and only this one is excluded, so a degenerate one-player game whose
corner resolution is $B^{2}$ with a run from another start is not ruled
out.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Suppose a one-player game has the walk $W$ as its run and let $s'$ be its
resolution with corners on the run. The outmaps of the $5$-cube agreeing
with the eleven prescribed values and satisfying the Szab\'o--Welzl
condition are $1897$ by the enumeration of \Cref{prop:hkfive}, of which
$875$ are acyclic --- $B^{2}$ among them --- and none is Holt--Klee
(reproduced here and by the route's auditor). Call the tie set $T$ of the run \emph{separable} if some linear $d$
has $d(D_{t,v})>0$ for all $(t,v)\in T$, which is condition $(\star)$ of
\Cref{lem:tie-perturb}; that lemma then makes the run the walk of a
Holt--Klee completion of $W$, so $T$ is not separable, and by
\Cref{cor:one-tie} its ties are neither absent nor all at one vertex with
one action. By \Cref{lem:tie-partner} a tie can sit only at an
incidence $(t,v)$ with $v\notin S_t$, $s'(\sigma_t\oplus e_v)=S_t\cup\{v\}$
and $\sigma_t\oplus e_v$ off the walk, and over the $875$ orientations
that set is $\emptyset$, $\{(5,2)\}$, $\{(7,3)\}$ or $\{(5,2),(7,3)\}$
and nothing else; so $T=\{(5,2),(7,3)\}$, with $\sigma_5=17$ and
$\sigma_7=6$, at different vertices, and \Cref{lem:two-ties} with
Gordan's alternative makes $T$ separable, a contradiction. (The same screen --- enumerate the
completions of a walk, discard the cyclic ones, return undecided if one
is Holt--Klee, else check every admissible tie set --- gives
\Cref{thm:no-seven} a second proof over the $768$ walks of length $7$ of
the $4$-cube, and applies to any walk one wishes to exclude.)
\end{proof}


\begin{proposition}[A run whose ties admit no linear tie-breaker]\label{prop:no-linear-tiebreak}
Let $G_{\mathrm{lin}}$ be the one-player stopping SSG with $m=4$ whose
harmonic normal form is, each row written
$(p^{v,a}_{1},\dots,p^{v,a}_{4}\,;\,q^{v,a})$,
\[
\begin{array}{r@{\quad}l@{\qquad}l}
 x_1: & (0,\tfrac{11}{16},\tfrac14,0\,;\,0) & (0,0,\tfrac18,0\,;\,\tfrac18)\\
 x_2: & (0,\tfrac5{16},0,\tfrac9{16}\,;\,0) & (0,0,\tfrac14,\tfrac{11}{16}\,;\,0)\\
 x_3: & (\tfrac5{16},0,\tfrac3{16},0\,;\,0) & (0,\tfrac9{16},0,\tfrac1{16}\,;\,0)\\
 x_4: & (0,0,0,0\,;\,0) & (\tfrac58,\tfrac3{16},0,0\,;\,\tfrac1{16}).
\end{array}
\]
Every row leaks to $t_0$, so $G_{\mathrm{lin}}$ is stopping; as a
fair-coin SSG it has $34$ vertices, $28$ of them average. Its all-switches
run from $\sigma_0=7$ is $7\to11\to13$, of length $2$, and carries
exactly two flat incidences, both at $x_2$: at $\sigma_0=7$ with action
$1$ and at $\sigma_2=13$ with action $0$. In exact arithmetic
$y^{15}-y^{13}=\tfrac{61360}{43111}\,(y^{7}-y^{5})$, so no linear
functional satisfies $(\star)$, whose two requirements here are
$d(y^{7}-y^{5})>0$ and $d(y^{13}-y^{15})>0$. The partial orientation
$(S,D)$ nevertheless has $96$ acyclic unique sink completions, $74$ of
them Holt--Klee, and $24$ of the completions agree with the run at its
three vertices, $17$ of those being Holt--Klee; so
$L\le h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(4)$ still holds here.
\end{proposition}

This is the shape Gordan's alternative predicts: $(\star)$ is infeasible
exactly when some nonnegative combination of the vectors
$y^{\sigma_t}-y^{\sigma_t[v]}$ vanishes, and the smallest way is two flat
incidences at the same Max vertex with opposite actions on two parallel
edges of a trapezoidal $2$-face of $X(G)$. A stronger condition that
perturbs the \emph{game} --- a direction $(\pi,\rho)$ of the rows,
nonnegative where the rows vanish, along which the first-order change of
every flat gap on the run is negative --- is feasible on
$G_{\mathrm{lin}}$ and, being an exact linear programme in $2m(m+1)$
variables, was tested on $4320$ runs of $191$ random degenerate games at
$m=4,5$ (random harmonic normal forms with prescribed ties, the route's
generator, not recorded here) without ever failing; but those runs have length at most $4$,
far below the ceilings, so this is a report and not evidence. Whether
every one-player stopping game satisfies
$L\le h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(|\Vmax|)$ remains open. Corner resolutions,
on the other hand, genuinely fail Holt--Klee: over $236$ random
degenerate instances at $m=4$, $3\,924$ of $17\,388$ corner resolutions
failed the unit-capacity max-flow test, so \Cref{thm:no-seven} cannot be
obtained by resolving, and \Cref{rem:oneplayer-lp}'s caution was
justified --- what \Cref{lem:tie-perturb} produces is a different
completion of the same partial orientation.

\begin{theorem}[Every zero-tie timer is linear]\label{thm:zero-timer}
Let $G$ be a stopping SSG with $\Vmin=\emptyset$ and $|\Vmax|=m$, let
$\sigma_0,\sigma_1,\dots$ be an all-switches run with switched sets
$S_0,S_1,\dots$, and let $u$ be a vertex with $\val_{\sigma_0}(u)=0$ and
$\val_{\sigma_T}(u)>0$, $T$ least. Then there are $T$ pairwise distinct
Max vertices $v_1,\dots,v_T$ such that the \emph{first} switch of $v_j$
occurs at round $T-j$. In particular $T\le m$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By \Cref{lem:switch} the vector $\val_{\sigma_t}$ is nondecreasing in
$t$, so $\val_{\sigma_t}(u)=0$ for $t<T$; and $\val_\sigma(v)=0$ exactly
when $t_1$ is unreachable from $v$ in the digraph $G_\sigma$ obtained by
deleting at each Max vertex the edge not taken (a vertex from which $t_1$
is reachable under $\sigma$ has positive value, and one from which it is
not lies in a trap of $G_\sigma$ and has value $0$ by
\Cref{thm:lfp-general}). Call such a vertex \emph{dead} at $\sigma$.

\emph{Claim.} If $w$ is dead at $\sigma_{s-1}$ and alive at $\sigma_s$
($1\le s\le T$) then there is a Max vertex $v$, dead at $\sigma_{s-1}$,
with $v\in S_{s-1}$, this being $v$'s first switch, and such that the
target $w'$ of $v$'s other action is alive at $\sigma_{s-1}$ and, when
$s\ge2$, dead at $\sigma_{s-2}$. Take a path $w=p_0\to\dots\to p_k=t_1$
in $G_{\sigma_s}$ and let $j$ be largest with $p_j$ dead at
$\sigma_{s-1}$; then $p_{j+1}$ is alive at $\sigma_{s-1}$, so the edge
$p_j\to p_{j+1}$ is absent from $G_{\sigma_{s-1}}$, which makes $v:=p_j$
a Max vertex with $v\in S_{s-1}$ and $\sigma_s(v)=p_{j+1}$. Had $v$
switched at some round $t<s-1$, its new action would have had positive
value at $\sigma_t$ (its old one had value $0$), so $\val_{\sigma_{t+1}}(v)>0$,
against $v$ dead at $\sigma_{s-1}$. If $s\ge2$ and $w':=p_{j+1}$ were
alive at $\sigma_{s-2}$, then at $\sigma_{s-2}$ the vertex $v$ would have
a dead chosen action and a live alternative, so $v\in S_{s-2}$, against
what was just shown. Iterating the claim from $w=u$, $s=T$ down to $s=1$
yields $v_1,\dots,v_T$ with first switches at the distinct rounds
$T-1,\dots,0$.
\end{proof}

The theorem is very likely folklore --- under policy improvement on a
reachability MDP the set of positive-value states grows by one backward
layer per round --- and is stated here because its sharpness matters
below. It is sharp: the staircase $T_k$ with $\Vmax=\{x_1,\dots,x_k\}$,
no average vertices, $x_1\to(t_0,t_1)$ and $x_j\to(t_0,x_{j-1})$ is
stopping, and from the all-first-actions start its all-switches run
switches $\{x_1\},\{x_2\},\dots,\{x_k\}$ in that order while
$x_{t+2},\dots,x_k$ are tied at $0$ at round $t$, so $x_k$ is a zero-tie
timer of length exactly $k=m$; the run length $m$ itself is of course
already the ladder's (\Cref{rem:ladder}), and the staircase is the
textbook two-action chain --- its content is the tie structure, not the
length.

\begin{corollary}[Gating is linear]\label{cor:gate}
Let a one-player stopping game be presented as a chain
$B_1\rhd B_2\rhd\dots\rhd B_r$ in which the only routes from $B_{i+1}$ to
$t_1$ pass through $B_i$, so that every vertex of $B_{i+1}$ is dead until
some vertex of $B_i$ wakes; suppose a block's vertices never switch while
dead, and that no vertex of $B_1,\dots,B_{r-1}$ switches after the round
at which $B_r$ wakes. With $m_i:=|\Vmax(B_i)|$ and $m=\sum_im_i$, the
all-switches run has length at most $(m-m_r)+h^{\ast}_{1}(m_r)$, which
for blocks with $m_i\le4$ is at most $m+2$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Let $T$ be the round at which $B_r$ wakes. By \Cref{thm:zero-timer}
there are $T$ distinct Max vertices whose first switches occur at rounds
$T-1,\dots,0$, none in $B_r$; so $T\le m-m_r$. After round $T$ only
$B_r$ moves, so the rest is an all-switches run of the subgame freezing
$\Vmax\setminus\Vmax(B_r)$ (\Cref{def:subcube}, \Cref{lem:subgame}), in
which the frozen vertices have coinciding out-edges and may be retyped as
average vertices; that is a one-player stopping game with $m_r$ Max
vertices, whose runs have length at most $h^{\ast}_{1}(m_r)$. The
numerical bound is $h^{\ast}_{1}(k)-k\le2$ for $k\le4$
(\Cref{cor:hstar-one}).
\end{proof}

The hypothesis is the point: it forbids in particular the offer-and-withdraw
mechanism that \Cref{rem:closed-now-or-never} identifies in every
published exponential lower bound, so the corollary rules out one-shot
gating and nothing more; it covers none of the mechanisms of
\Cref{sec:gap}. The bound is attained: placing the leaky staircase $T_s$
(each second action leaking $\tfrac12$ to $t_0$) beside a height-$6$
one-player game $B$ of \Cref{rem:hk-survey} and routing every
$t_1$-mass of $B$'s rows to $x_s$ gives, for $m=s+4$, a one-player
stopping game on $N=2m+115$ vertices whose run from the all-first-actions
start has length exactly $m+2$ --- the block is tied at $0$ for $s$
rounds and then runs $B$'s run unchanged, since its values are
$B$'s scaled by $\val(x_s)$ --- measured from the explicit game at
$m=4,8,12,16,20$ with run lengths $6,10,14,18,22$. So ties buy
sequencing, one round per Max vertex, and never more; a one-player
family beating $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}$ must not be built from zero-tie
gating.

\subsection{Deformed cubes, and why the blow-up leaves the Holt--Klee class}\label{sec:deformed}

\Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp} turns the one-player half of the pivot into a
question about polytopes, and the classical source of long simplex paths
on cubes is the Klee--Minty cube and its descendants. This subsection,
from the round-$15$ howard-cube route, shows that the whole of that family
is bottom-antipodally flat, and locates the two faces at which the
blow-up of \Cref{thm:blowup} leaves the Holt--Klee class.

\begin{definition}[Deformed products]\label{def:deformed}
Let $P_i=\{y_i\in\mathbb R^{n_i}:C_iy_i\le d_i\}$, $i=1,\dots,k$, be
polytopes and $D_i$ matrices. The \emph{deformed product}
$P_1\ltimes\cdots\ltimes P_k$ is
$\{y=(y_1,\dots,y_k):C_iy_i\le d_i+D_i(y_1,\dots,y_{i-1}),\ i\le k\}$,
assumed to be a polytope whose face lattice is that of
$P_1\times\cdots\times P_k$, the facet cut by the $r$-th row of the $i$-th
block corresponding to the facet of $P_i$ cut by the $r$-th row of $C_i$
(Amenta and Ziegler, from memory, unchecked against the source). When
every $P_i$ is a segment this is a \emph{deformed cube}: after
normalising, $l_i(x_{<i})\le x_i\le u_i(x_{<i})$ with $l_i,u_i$ affine and
$l_i<u_i$ on the polytope; the Klee--Minty cube
$0\le x_1\le1$, $\varepsilon x_{i-1}\le x_i\le1-\varepsilon x_{i-1}$ with
objective $x_d$, and Chv\'atal's form of it, are deformed cubes.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[The last factor is rigid (the defining property of Amenta and Ziegler's construction, from memory)]\label{lem:deformed-rigid}
Let $P=P_1\ltimes\cdots\ltimes P_k$ be a deformed product of simple
polytopes and fix vertices $w_1,\dots,w_{k-1}$ of the first $k-1$ factors.
The fibre of $P$ over them projects isomorphically onto
$Q(w):=\{y_k:C_ky_k\le d_k+D_k(y_{<k}(w))\}$, which has the normal fan of
$P_k$; so for each edge of the combinatorial cube of $P_k$ the vector
joining its ends in $Q(w)$ is a positive multiple of the corresponding
vector in $P_k$, and a fixed functional $c_k$ induces the same
orientation on every fibre.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
At a point of the fibre the tight constraints of the first $k-1$ blocks
are those tight at $w_1,\dots,w_{k-1}$, and each block being simple they
determine $y_i$ as an affine function of $y_{<i}$, so $y_{<k}$ is the
constant $y_{<k}(w)$ and the fibre is $Q(w)$. $Q(w)$ and $P_k$ are cut by
the same normal vectors with the same face lattice and facet labelling,
so their normal fans coincide; an edge with tight set $T\cup\{j_0\}$ at
one end spans $\ker\{C_r:r\in T\}$ in both, and its direction sign is
fixed by $C_{j_0}$, independently of $w$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Deformed products are bottom-antipodally flat]\label{thm:deformed-flat}
Let $P=P_1\ltimes\cdots\ltimes P_k$ be a deformed product whose factors
are polytopes combinatorially equivalent to cubes of dimensions
$m_1,\dots,m_k$, so that $P$ is combinatorially the $d$-cube,
$d=\sum m_i$, and let $c$ be a linear objective taking distinct values at
the ends of every edge. Then the bottom-antipodal height of the
orientation $c$ induces on $P$ is at most
$\sum_ih^{\ast}_{\mathrm{LP}}(m_i)\le\sum_ih^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(m_i)$,
where $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{LP}}(m)$ is the greatest bottom-antipodal height
of an orientation induced by a linear objective on a polytope
combinatorially an $m$-cube. In particular a deformed cube has
bottom-antipodal height at most $d$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Orient every edge towards the larger value of $c$, as in
\Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp}; a generic objective then induces on a polytope
combinatorially a cube an acyclic unique sink orientation (each face has
the unique maximiser of $c$ as its sink, and $c$ increases along every
arc), so all walks below terminate. Induct on $k$. By \Cref{lem:deformed-rigid} the
last block of the outmap is $\sigma_k(w_k(y))$ for one orientation
$\sigma_k$ of the $m_k$-cube, induced by $c_k$ on $P_k$, of height at
most $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{LP}}(m_k)$; so the bottom-antipodal step acts on
the last block autonomously, and after at most that many steps the last
block sits at $\sigma_k$'s sink $w^{\ast}_k$ and stays. On the face
$G:=\{w_k(y)=w^{\ast}_k\}$ the $m_k$ tight constraints of the last block
express $y_k$ affinely in $y_{<k}$, so $G$ projects injectively and
affinely onto the deformed product of the first $k-1$ factors --- the two
have the same vertices, computed by the same recursion, and $G$ is a face
of $P$ whose face lattice is, by the standing hypothesis on $P$, that of
$P_1\times\dots\times P_{k-1}\times\{w^{\ast}_k\}$, so the image satisfies
the same hypothesis --- with $c$ becoming a linear functional there, and
the walk continues as the bottom-antipodal walk of that orientation. For a deformed cube every
$m_i=1$: the last coordinate is uniform, one step enters the facet it
points to, and induction gives $d$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The classical cubes, measured]\label{prop:km-measured}
In exact rational arithmetic the Klee--Minty cube with
$\varepsilon\in\{\tfrac14,\tfrac1{10}\}$ for $d\le12$ and Chv\'atal's form
of it for $d\le10$ induce acyclic unique sink orientations whose longest
directed path has $2^{d}-1$ edges --- a Hamiltonian monotone path, which
is why the classical pivot rules are exponential on them --- and whose
bottom-antipodal height is exactly $d$, the bound of
\Cref{thm:deformed-flat}. For the Klee--Minty cube the outmap is
$s(b)=\{i:b_i\equiv b_{i+1}+\dots+b_d\pmod2\}$, independently of
$\varepsilon\in(0,\tfrac12)$, with sink $(0,\dots,0,1)$ (checked against
the objective values for $d\le8$).
\end{proposition}

For a deformed cube the whole content is three lines: a $d$-edge joins
$(x_{<d},l_d)$ to $(x_{<d},u_d)$ with $l_d<u_d$, so the objective changes
by $c_d(u_d-l_d)$, a sign independent of $x_{<d}$; the last coordinate is
combed, one bottom-antipodal step enters the facet it points to, and that
facet is again a deformed cube. So the standard machine for producing long
monotone paths on cubes is useless for the pivot of \Cref{cor:f-auso}: a
deformed product can only add its factors' ceilings $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{LP}}(m_i)$
(nothing in the argument controls the lower factors' own orientations, so
the bound is a sum of ceilings, not of heights). The occupancy polytope $X(G)$ of
\Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp} is not a deformed cube --- a uniform coordinate
forces $h\le1+h^{\ast}(m-1)$, which is $5$ at $m=4$, while one-player
games with $|\Vmax|=4$ and runs of length $6$ exist
(\Cref{rem:hk-survey}) --- so the theorem does not reach the pivot; it
closes the classical arsenal, and it is the only positive statement we
have about bottom-antipodal walks on LP orientations. It is a closed
construction avenue and not a barrier in the sense of \Cref{sec:gap}: it
covers none of the mechanisms M1--M6 or \Cref{def:bsi}, and the published
exponential lower bounds are built from games, not from deformed
products.

\begin{lemma}[Two stacking rules]\label{lem:stack}
Let $F$ and $G$ be unique sink orientations of the $2$-cube and
$\varphi:\{0,1\}^{2}\to\{0,1\}$; orient the $3$-cube by $G$ on the bottom
square $\{x_3=0\}$, $F$ on the top, and the vertical edge at $v$ upward
iff $\varphi(v)=1$. (a) If $G=F$ the result is a unique sink orientation,
and over all $192$ pairs $(F,\varphi)$ it is acyclic and Holt--Klee in
exactly $168$: precisely when it is not the case that $\varphi$ takes the
value $1$ exactly twice and $\varphi(\mathrm{src}F)=\varphi(\mathrm{snk}F)$.
(b) If $\varphi\equiv0$ all $144$ pairs $(F,G)$ give an acyclic unique
sink orientation, and exactly $16$ fail Holt--Klee: precisely when both
$F$ and $G$ have their source adjacent to their sink and the two
source--sink edges are opposite edges of the square. (Exhaustive, with the
maximum-flow test; reproduced here.)
\end{lemma}

\begin{lemma}[The $3$-faces of the blow-up]\label{lem:blowup-faces}
Let $s$ be an acyclic unique sink orientation of the $m$-cube with height
function $h$, let $z$ be any translation vector and $B=B(s,z)$ the outmap
defined as in \Cref{thm:blowup} but with inner part $s(v\oplus z)$ on the
layer $(0,0)$ --- the theorem's unique-sink and acyclicity arguments use
only that $v\mapsto s(v\oplus z)$ is an acyclic unique sink orientation,
so $B(s,z)$ is one for every $z$, which is the generality the Lean
formalisation proves; $B(s)=B(s,o\oplus u)$. Let
$F$ be a $2$-face of $s$ on directions $\{i,j\}$ and $G$ the orientation
$v\mapsto s(v\oplus z)$ read on the same square. Then the $3$-face of $B$
on $\{i,j,\alpha\}$ with $\beta=1$ is the stack of $F$ over $F$ with
$\varphi=h\bmod2$, the one on $\{i,j,\beta\}$ with $\alpha=1$ the same
stack with $1-\varphi$, and the $3$-faces on $\{i,j,\alpha\}$ with
$\beta=0$ and on $\{i,j,\beta\}$ with $\alpha=0$ are the stack of $F$ over
$G$ with every vertical edge pointing towards the layer $(0,0)$.
Consequently $B(s,z)$ is Holt--Klee only if, for every $2$-face $F$,
\emph{(P)} $h\bmod2$ is not balanced on $F$ or
$h(\mathrm{src}F)\not\equiv h(\mathrm{snk}F)\pmod2$, and
\emph{(T)} $F$ and $G$ do not both have source adjacent to sink with
opposite source--sink edges.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The inner part of $B$ is $s$ on the three layers other than $(0,0)$ and
$s(\cdot\oplus z)$ there. By the table of \Cref{thm:blowup}, $O_{01}$
contains $\alpha$ exactly when $h(v)$ is odd and $O_{11}$ contains it
exactly when $h(v)$ is even, so the $\alpha$-edge between the layers
$(0,1)$ and $(1,1)$ points up exactly at odd $h(v)$; likewise the
$\beta$-edge between $(1,0)$ and $(1,1)$ points up exactly at even
$h(v)$. And $O_{00}=\emptyset$ while $O_{10}\ni\alpha$ and $O_{01}\ni\beta$
for both parities, so the edges into the layer $(0,0)$ all point towards
it. The two conditions are \Cref{lem:stack} read off these faces, and
Holt--Klee is inherited by faces.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The parity obstruction is intrinsic to the seed]\label{cor:blowup-parity}
Condition \emph{(P)} does not involve $z$. Among the acyclic unique sink
orientations of the $3$-cube no class of height $4$ satisfies it on every
$2$-face, and the greatest height of a class that does is $3$; among the
$12640$ classes of the $4$-cube the height-$7$ class does not satisfy it,
and of the $56$ Holt--Klee classes of height $6$ exactly $15$ do. So for a
seed of maximal height at $m=3$ or $m=4$, $B(s,z)$ fails Holt--Klee for
every $z$. (Census computations, reproduced here.)
\end{corollary}

\begin{corollary}[The translation obstruction]\label{cor:blowup-transl}
If $z=e_i$ is a single coordinate and $B(s,e_i)$ satisfies \emph{(T)} on
every $2$-face containing the direction $i$, then coordinate $i$ is
uniform in $s$ --- all its edges point the same way. Neither outer
coordinate of a blow-up is uniform, since the $\alpha$-edge between the
layers $(0,1)$ and $(1,1)$ points up exactly at odd inner height and $h$
takes both parities; hence the canonical iterate of the blow-up --- the
one started at the walk's own vertex $u$, whose translation vector is a
single outer coordinate of the previous level
(\Cref{rem:blowup-measured}) --- fails Holt--Klee from the second level
on. The other vertices of maximal height give translation vectors of
weight $2$ and $3$, to which this argument does not apply; but \emph{(P)}
fails for $B^{1}$, $B^{2}$ and $B^{3}$ themselves (exact arithmetic), so
by \Cref{cor:blowup-parity} $B(B^{k},z)$ fails Holt--Klee for $k=1,2,3$,
every $z$ and every choice of $u$. Whether \emph{(P)} fails at every level
is not proved. All of this concerns the parity readout of
\Cref{thm:blowup}; with the outer pair driven by another readout the
blow-up can double and stay Holt--Klee, at $m=4$ and $m=5$
(\Cref{prop:blowup-readout,prop:hk-doubling-measured}).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For $z=e_i$ and a $2$-face $F$ on $\{i,j\}$, $G$ is $F$ translated by the
local coordinate $i$, so the source--sink pair of $G$ is that of $F$
translated by $e_i$; the two pairs are the opposite edges of the square
exactly when the source and sink of $F$ are adjacent along $j$, which for
a unique sink orientation of the square happens iff its two $i$-edges
point in opposite ways. So \emph{(T)} on all faces containing $i$ says
that the $i$-edges are oriented alike on each of the facets
$\{v_i=0\}$, $\{v_i=1\}$, that is, $i$ is uniform.
\end{proof}

So the blow-up leaves the Holt--Klee class for two reasons at once ---
the outer square reads the parity of the inner height, and a $2$-face on
which that parity is balanced with equal ends carries two disjoint
monotone paths where three are needed; and the layer $(0,0)$ carries a
translate, which on a $2$-face moves the source--sink edge to the opposite
edge --- and the operation $D$ of \Cref{prop:D-quadratic} for the same
reasons. Over every acyclic unique sink orientation of the $m$-cube for
$m\le4$ and every $z$, the Holt--Klee blow-ups $B(s,z)$ reach heights at
most $4,6,8,12$ at dimensions $3,4,5,6$, against
$h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}=4,6,11,\ge14$ (exhaustive, the route's
computation, the last value from a scan that finished after its report
and corrected it; the $14$ from \Cref{prop:hk-records}): at dimension $6$
the best Holt--Klee blow-up of this rule family, from a seed of height $5$
translated by the all-ones vector, reaches $12$, two below the height
$14$ of \Cref{prop:hk-records}, which is itself a blow-up with a readout
other than the parity (\Cref{prop:hk-doubling-measured}). Nothing here shows that no
dimension-raising doubling preserves Holt--Klee, and $11\ge2\cdot4+2$
leaves room for one;
that statement, $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(m+2)\ge2h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(m)+2$
for every $m$, is the exact one an LP-realisable exponential family would
need first.

\begin{proposition}[Explicit one-player runs at five and six Max vertices]\label{prop:oneplayer-runs}
Present a one-player stopping game by its harmonic normal form, a row
$(p^{v,a}_1,\dots,p^{v,a}_m;q^{v,a})$ of nonnegative integers over a
denominator $D$ for each action, with $\sum_jp^{v,a}_j+q^{v,a}<D$ so that
the action leaks to $t_0$; by \Cref{lem:dyadic-row} every such form is the
normal form of a stopping one-player SSG on at most $2m(m+1)\log_2D$
average vertices, $720$ and $1008$ below. With $D=4096$ the rows, in the order $(x_1,0),(x_1,1),(x_2,0),\dots$,
\[
\begin{array}{llll}
(1484,1821,561,13,58;\,0) & (28,0,0,4054,0;\,12) & (1144,601,636,1393,5;\,0) & (0,950,190,76,2753;\,5)\\
(1,1359,1854,11,76;\,387) & (3,7,2929,22,1123;\,1) & (1439,0,0,2655,0;\,0) & (719,1109,277,265,1435;\,20)\\
(840,3,3222,3,0;\,0) & (14,2,1,1910,1829;\,5)
\end{array}
\]
give a nondegenerate game with $|\Vmax|=5$ whose improvement orientation
is the Holt--Klee orientation
\[
\begin{aligned}
 (&11,4,1,22,15,2,13,0,3,10,8,9,23,14,21,12,\\
  &17,16,19,6,31,20,29,18,24,25,26,27,7,28,5,30)
\end{aligned}
\]
and whose all-switches run from $12$ is $12,27,0,11,2,3,21,1,5,7$, of
length $9$; and the rows
\[
\begin{array}{lll}
(3558,515,0,1,0,1;\,20) & (2327,0,1583,0,119,0;\,65) & (4060,33,0,1,1,0;\,0)\\
(1,0,0,0,0,4069;\,0) & (213,453,1485,0,955,1;\,892) & (0,1,0,4086,0,1;\,2)\\
(359,221,2,0,1478,2;\,1850) & (0,0,0,0,1,4093;\,0) & (0,0,3318,482,1,0;\,2)\\
(2310,1261,0,0,0,0;\,0) & (7,9,0,973,0,3085;\,19) & (0,1,3077,0,0,0;\,901)
\end{array}
\]
give one with $|\Vmax|=6$ and a run $25,14,33,6,37,46,45,44,16,56,24,28,12$
of length $12$. Writing $h^{\ast}_{1}(m)$ as in \Cref{cor:hstar-one},
\[
  h^{\ast}_{1}(m)\ \ge\ 1,\ 2,\ 4,\ 6,\ 9,\ 12\qquad(m=1,\dots,6),
\]
with equality for $m\le4$, and $h^{\ast}_{1}$ is nondecreasing (adjoin a
Max vertex over $t_0$ and a fresh coin of intermediate value that no other
vertex sees). Every value, comparison, tie count, run length and the
Holt--Klee property were recomputed here in exact arithmetic.
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}[The four ceilings, as far as they are known]\label{rem:four-ceilings}
\[
\begin{array}{l|ccccccc}
m & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6 & 7\\ \hline
h^{\ast}(m) & 1 & 2 & 4 & 7 & 12 & \ge16 & \ge26\\
h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(m) & 1 & 2 & 4 & 6 & 11 & \ge14 & \ge20\\
h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{LP}}(m) & 1 & 2 & 4 & 6 & \ge10 & \ge12 & \ge13\\
h^{\ast}_{1}(m) & 1 & 2 & 4 & 6 & \ge10 & \ge12 & \ge13
\end{array}
\]
The first row is \Cref{prop:auso-census,prop:hstar-five,thm:blowup}; the
second \Cref{prop:auso-census,prop:hkfive} and, at $m=6,7$, the explicit
orientations of \Cref{prop:hk-records}, with $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(m)\ge m+13$
for $m\ge7$ by the sink lift of \Cref{prop:sink-lift} (the product of
\Cref{lem:hstar-super} taken with the height-$11$ witness of
\Cref{prop:hkfive} as its \emph{first} block, the $1$-cube as its second
and $z=1$ gives only $12$, and once more $13$
(verified here; with the blocks in the other order the product has height
$12$ but is not Holt--Klee, and with Holt--Klee factors of maximal
