Holt--Klee height --- the realised height-$6$ class at $m=4$, the
height-$11$ witness at $m=5$, $z$ as the lemma prescribes --- the
products at block dimensions $(4,4)$, $(4,5)$, $(5,4)$ and $(5,5)$ all
fail Holt--Klee on a $3$-face (the route's auditor, reproduced by this
round's), so \Cref{lem:hstar-super} preserves Holt--Klee for some pairs
of factors and not others and no doubling follows); the last two rows are
\Cref{cor:hstar-one,prop:oneplayer-runs,prop:hstar-one-five} together with
$h^{\ast,\mathrm{nd}}_{1}\le h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{LP}}\le h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}$
for the ceiling $h^{\ast,\mathrm{nd}}_{1}$ over \emph{nondegenerate}
one-player games (\Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp} needs nondegeneracy;
$h^{\ast}_{1}$ itself allows ties, and whether it obeys
$h^{\ast}_{1}\le h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}$ is the question left open after
\Cref{thm:no-seven}, settled there for $m\le4$ only), every lower bound
of the last row being attained by a nondegenerate game
(\Cref{prop:oneplayer-runs,prop:hstar-one-five,prop:oneplayer-plus-one}
state this of their witnesses), so that the third row inherits it, and
the monotonicity of both in $m$ (the game
of \Cref{prop:oneplayer-runs} adjoining a Max vertex over $t_0$ and a
fresh coin stays nondegenerate), the lower bounds being what a search
found and not claimed tight, the $m=7$ entries of the last two rows
coming from the plus-one law of \Cref{prop:oneplayer-plus-one}; beyond
the table, $h^{\ast}_{1}(8)\ge13$, $h^{\ast}_{1}(m)\ge m+6$ for $m\ge6$
and $h^{\ast}_{1}(7k)\ge12k$ for every $k$
(\Cref{prop:hstar-one-eight,prop:oneplayer-plus-one,cor:stack-family}). The one-player ceiling is strictly
below $h^{\ast}$ at $m=4$; at $m\ge5$ the last three rows are lower
bounds only, so the table does not yet distinguish
$h^{\ast}_{1}(m)\approx2m$ from $h^{\ast}_{1}(m)\approx c^{m}$; whether
$h^{\ast}_{1}$ grows superpolynomially, and is attained on $m^{O(1)}$
vertices, is the one-player half of the pivot, which is Melekopoglou and
Condon's question (\Cref{rem:ladder}).
\end{remark}

\subsubsection*{Gluing facets, the sink lift, and the Holt--Klee ceiling at $m=6,7$}

The round-$16$ Holt--Klee route raised the second row of
\Cref{rem:four-ceilings} at $m=6,7$, proved a ``plus one per dimension''
law for the Holt--Klee row and, by a one-player gadget, for the one-player
row, and showed that the blow-up doubles while
staying Holt--Klee once its outer pair reads something other than the
parity --- at $m=4$ and $m=5$, where a readout-free necessary condition
then stops the iteration.

\begin{lemma}[The facet decomposition of a cube orientation]\label{lem:facet-gluing}
Write the vertices of the $(m+1)$-cube as $(v,b)$ with $v\in\{0,1\}^{m}$,
$b\in\{0,1\}$, the new direction being $m$. For orientations $s_0,s_1$ of
the $m$-cube and $\varphi:\{0,1\}^{m}\to\{0,1\}$ put
\[
  (s_0\!*\!s_1\!*\!\varphi)(v,b)\ :=\ s_b(v)\ \cup\ \{m\ :\ \varphi(v)\ne b\}.
\]
Every orientation of the $(m+1)$-cube is $s_0\!*\!s_1\!*\!\varphi$ for a
unique triple, $s_b$ being its restriction to the facet $\{x_m=b\}$ and
$\varphi(v)=1$ meaning that the edge in direction $m$ at $v$ points away
from the facet $\{x_m=0\}$. Let
\[
 E(s_0,s_1):=\bigl\{\{v,w\}: v\ne w,\ (s_0(v)\oplus s_1(w))\cap(v\oplus w)=\emptyset
   \ \text{ or }\ (s_0(w)\oplus s_1(v))\cap(v\oplus w)=\emptyset\bigr\}.
\]
Then $s_0\!*\!s_1\!*\!\varphi$ is a unique sink orientation if and only if
$s_0$ and $s_1$ are unique sink orientations and $\varphi$ is constant on
every connected component of $E(s_0,s_1)$. If $s_0=s_1$ then
$E(s_0,s_1)=\emptyset$ and every $\varphi$ is admissible. Moreover the
bottom-antipodal step is
\[
  (v,b)\ \longmapsto\ \bigl(v\oplus s_b(v),\ \varphi(v)\bigr),
\]
so the chart used at time $t+1$ is $\varphi$ of the vertex at time $t$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Apply the Szab\'o--Welzl condition of \Cref{def:improvement-uso} to each
pair. Two vertices in one facet are separated exactly when that facet's
orientation is a unique sink orientation. For $(v,0)$ and $(v,1)$ the
direction-$m$ bits are $[\varphi(v)\ne0]$ and $[\varphi(v)\ne1]$, which
always differ. For $(v,0)$ and $(w,1)$ with $v\ne w$ the symmetric
difference meets $(v\oplus w)\cup\{m\}$ in the bit $m$ exactly when
$\varphi(v)=\varphi(w)$, and inside $v\oplus w$ exactly when
$(s_0(v)\oplus s_1(w))\cap(v\oplus w)\ne\emptyset$; the pair $(w,0)$,
$(v,1)$ gives the transposed condition. So the only failures are pairs
$\{v,w\}\in E$ with $\varphi(v)\ne\varphi(w)$. When $s_0=s_1=s$ the
Szab\'o--Welzl condition for $s$ makes both intersections nonempty, so $E$
is empty. The step formula: the outer bit of $(v,b)$ is out exactly when
$\varphi(v)\ne b$, and $b\oplus[\varphi(v)\ne b]=\varphi(v)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The sink lift: $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(m+1)\ge h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(m)+1$]\label{prop:sink-lift}
Let $s$ be an acyclic unique sink orientation of the $m$-cube with sink $o$
and bottom-antipodal height $h$, and let
$L(s):=s\!*\!s\!*\!\mathbf 1_{\{o\}}$ be the orientation of the
$(m+1)$-cube with $s$ on both facets and the new edge at $v$ pointing away
from the facet $\{x_m=0\}$ exactly at $v=o$. Then
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $L(s)$ is an acyclic unique sink orientation with sink $(o,1)$
and bottom-antipodal height exactly $h+1$;
\item[(ii)] $L(s)$ is Holt--Klee if and only if $s$ is.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(m+1)\ge h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(m)+1$
and $h^{\ast}(m+1)\ge h^{\ast}(m)+1$ for every $m$. Up to relabelling the
new coordinate, $L(s)$ is the product of \Cref{lem:hstar-super} with the
$1$-cube as second block and $z=1$, so (i) is that lemma; what is new is
(ii), that this one product preserves Holt--Klee in both directions.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
$L(s)$ is a unique sink orientation by \Cref{lem:facet-gluing}, the two
facets being equal. Acyclicity: every edge in a direction $<m$ projects to
an edge of $s$ and every edge in direction $m$ projects to a loop, so a
directed cycle projects to a closed directed walk of the acyclic digraph
of $s$, which is constant; the cycle then lies in one fibre
$\{v\}\times\{0,1\}$ and would need $\varphi(v)=1$ and $\varphi(v)=0$. The
sink: $s(o)=\emptyset$ and the direction-$m$ edge at $(o,1)$ is in, since
$\varphi(o)=1=b$.

Height. By \Cref{lem:facet-gluing} the step is
$(v,b)\mapsto(v\oplus s(v),\varphi(v))$, so the first coordinate performs
the bottom-antipodal walk of $s$ whatever $b$ does; from a start with
$h(v_0)=h$ it reaches $o$ after $h$ steps, with $b=\varphi(v_{h-1})=0$
because $v_{h-1}\ne o$, and one further step goes to $(o,1)$, which is the
sink. So the height is $h+1$, and it is not more because the first
coordinate is autonomous.

Holt--Klee. A facet of a Holt--Klee orientation is Holt--Klee, which gives
one direction. For the other, let $F$ be a face of the $m$-cube of
dimension $d\ge1$, so that $F\times\{0,1\}$ is a face of dimension $d+1$;
faces contained in a facet are copies of faces of $s$ and are Holt--Klee
by hypothesis. Let $a$ be the source and $z$ the sink of $s$ on $F$; note
that $o$, having no out-edge at all, is the sink of every face containing
it. Fix $d$ internally vertex-disjoint monotone $a\to z$ paths
$P_1,\dots,P_d$ in $F$, which Holt--Klee supplies; at most one of them is
a single edge, and we index so that $P_1$ is that one if it occurs.

\emph{Case $o\notin F$.} Then $\varphi\equiv0$ on $F$ and every edge in
direction $m$ points from $(v,1)$ to $(v,0)$; the source of
$F\times\{0,1\}$ is $(a,1)$ and its sink is $(z,0)$. Take
\[
\begin{aligned}
 Q_1&:(a,1)\to(a,0)\to[P_1\ \text{below}]\to(z,0), &
 Q_2&:(a,1)\to[P_1\ \text{above}]\to(z,1)\to(z,0),\\
 Q_{i+1}&:(a,1)\to[P_i\ \text{above, as far as } w_i]\to(w_i,0)\to[P_i\ \text{below}]\to(z,0)
   &&(2\le i\le d),
\end{aligned}
\]
where $w_i$ is an interior vertex of $P_i$, which exists because
$P_2,\dots,P_d$ have length at least $2$. These are $d+1$ monotone paths;
$Q_1$ and $Q_2$ use $P_1$ in different copies, $Q_{i+1}$ uses only
interior vertices of $P_i$ in the two copies together with $(w_i,0)$, and
$w_i\notin\{a,z\}$, so they are internally vertex-disjoint.

\emph{Case $o\in F$.} Then $z=o$, $\varphi=\mathbf 1_{\{o\}}$ on $F$, the
edge in direction $m$ points up at $o$ and down elsewhere, the source is
$(a,1)$ and the sink is $(o,1)$. Take the $d$ paths
$(a,1)\to[P_i\ \text{above}]\to(o,1)$ together with
$(a,1)\to(a,0)\to[P_1\ \text{below}]\to(o,0)\to(o,1)$: again $d+1$
monotone, internally vertex-disjoint paths.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[$h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(6)\ge14$ and $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(7)\ge20$]\label{prop:hk-records}
The outmap
\[
\begin{aligned}
 s_6=(&5,10,15,4,9,14,11,8,13,6,7,12,1,0,2,3,\\
      &48,17,51,50,54,61,60,55,30,25,24,59,26,53,52,63,\\
      &32,49,35,34,38,45,44,39,62,57,56,43,58,37,36,47,\\
      &16,33,19,18,22,29,28,23,46,41,40,27,42,21,20,31)
\end{aligned}
\]
of the $6$-cube and
\[
\begin{aligned}
 s_7=(&7,4,21,6,10,11,17,24,29,12,31,14,3,0,25,2,\\
      &23,18,5,20,19,22,8,9,15,28,13,30,27,26,1,16,\\
      &56,113,43,42,102,117,100,103,98,121,32,99,46,127,44,125,\\
      &41,40,54,51,116,101,50,119,48,97,58,59,62,109,60,111,\\
      &120,81,107,106,70,85,68,71,66,89,96,67,110,95,108,93,\\
      &105,104,118,115,84,69,114,87,112,65,122,123,126,77,124,79,\\
      &88,49,75,74,38,53,36,39,34,57,64,35,78,63,76,61,\\
      &73,72,86,83,52,37,82,55,80,33,90,91,94,45,92,47)
\end{aligned}
\]
of the $7$-cube are acyclic unique sink orientations satisfying Holt--Klee
on every face, of bottom-antipodal heights $14$ and $20$; the maximal
walks are
\[
 21,40,22,42,18,33,16,32,0,5,11,7,15,12,13
\]
for $s_6$ (sink $13$; also from $29,53,61$) and
\[
 45,82,36,66,41,80,57,88,40,74,42,10,21,3,5,14,23,30,31,15,13
\]
for $s_7$ (sink $13$; also from $47,109,111$). Hence
\[
  h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(6)\ \ge\ 14,\qquad
  h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(7)\ \ge\ 20,
\]
and with \Cref{prop:sink-lift},
$h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(m)\ge m+13$ for every $m\ge7$. An intermediate
record found by a different method, and kept because its shape is
informative, is the Holt--Klee orientation of the
$7$-cube of height $17$
\[
\begin{aligned}
 (&64,29,31,94,12,51,34,25,8,53,54,55,52,59,42,49,\\
  &28,3,38,9,26,5,24,7,48,15,46,33,36,11,50,13,\\
  &44,45,62,63,40,19,10,41,32,23,2,1,6,27,20,21,\\
  &60,61,14,47,58,43,56,57,22,35,17,16,18,37,4,39,\\
  &0,65,67,30,71,76,93,98,79,72,73,118,75,116,101,106,\\
  &95,92,89,102,115,90,77,88,87,112,117,110,123,100,113,114,\\
  &127,108,125,126,105,104,107,74,103,96,97,66,99,70,85,84,\\
  &109,124,111,78,83,122,121,120,119,86,81,80,91,82,69,68),
\end{aligned}
\]
whose maximal walk is
$68,3,93,57,26,52,14,36,12,56,46,58,43,42,40,8,0,64$ and whose two facets
are Holt--Klee orientations of the $6$-cube of height $13$ with a common
sink.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Finite computations, made twice in independent implementations (a C engine
and a Python one): the Szab\'o--Welzl condition on all pairs, acyclicity by
depth-first search, the bottom-antipodal heights of all $2^{m}$ starts,
and Holt--Klee by a unit vertex-capacity maximum flow on every face of
dimension at least two ($3^{6}=729$ and $3^{7}=2187$ faces). The
Holt--Klee test was validated on the twelve unique sink orientations of the
$2$-cube, on the $656$ Holt--Klee orientations of the $3$-cube
(\Cref{prop:auso-census}) and on the non-Holt--Klee outmap of
$G^{\sharp}$, where it reproduces the witness $3$-face
$\{x_1,x_2,x_4\}$ at bitmask $4$ of \Cref{cor:seven-two-player}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The generalised blow-up: an arbitrary readout]\label{prop:blowup-readout}
For an orientation $s$ of the $m$-cube, $z\in\{0,1\}^{m}$ and a
\emph{readout} $\varphi:\{0,1\}^{m}\to\{0,1\}$ let $B_\varphi(s,z)$ be the
outmap of the $(m+2)$-cube given by the table of \Cref{thm:blowup} with
$\varphi(v)$ in place of the parity of $h(v)$. Then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] If $s$ is an acyclic unique sink orientation then so is
$B_\varphi(s,z)$, for \emph{every} $z$ and every $\varphi$ (the
generality that the Lean formalisation of \Cref{thm:blowup} proves,
recorded here in the readout reading).
\item[(b)] If $\varphi$ alternates along a bottom-antipodal walk of $s$
from a vertex $u$ of height $h$ to the sink $o$, and $z=o\oplus u$, then
$B_\varphi(s,z)$ has height at least $2h+2$, with equality when $u$ has
maximal height (\Cref{prop:blowup-height}); for a non-maximal $u$ the
height can exceed $2h+2$ (exhaustively at $m=3$, the route's auditor).
\item[(c)] $B_\varphi(s,z)$ is Holt--Klee only if, for every $2$-face $F$
of $s$, $\varphi$ is unbalanced on $F$ or $\varphi(\mathrm{src}F)\ne
\varphi(\mathrm{snk}F)$ \emph{(P$_\varphi$)}, and the translation
condition \emph{(T)} of \Cref{lem:blowup-faces} holds; \emph{(T)} does not
involve $\varphi$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(a) The Szab\'o--Welzl condition. Two vertices in one layer are separated
by the layer's inner orientation. For two vertices in different layers with
the same inner $v$, the outer outmaps of \Cref{thm:blowup} differ on the
layer difference in all eight cases. For different layers and different
inner vertices, the same check leaves only the pairs of layers
$\{(1,0),(0,1)\}$, $\{(1,0),(1,1)\}$ and $\{(0,1),(1,1)\}$ at one readout
combination each, and there both layers carry $s$, which separates them.
Acyclicity, as in the proof of \Cref{thm:blowup}: no outer edge leaves the
layer $(0,0)$, whose inner orientation is the translate of $s$, so a
directed cycle meeting $(0,0)$ would lie in it; a cycle avoiding $(0,0)$
moves its inner coordinate only along edges of the one acyclic
orientation $s$ carried by the other three layers, so its inner vertex is
constant, and it would then lie in three vertices of the square of
layers, which contain no cycle.
(b) The layer dynamics of the table sends $(1,0)\mapsto(0,1)$ at
$\varphi=0$ and $(0,1)\mapsto(1,0)$ at $\varphi=1$, and drops to $(0,0)$
in the two remaining cases; so an alternating readout keeps the walk in
the layers $(1,0),(0,1)$ for the $h$ steps of the inner walk and for one
more, then enters $(0,0)$, where the inner map is $s(\cdot\oplus z)$ and
the inner vertex $o$ reads as $o\oplus z=u$, so the inner walk runs again,
$h$ steps. Total $2h+2$.
(c) The $3$-faces of $B_\varphi$ listed in \Cref{lem:blowup-faces} are
stacks with $\varphi$, with $1-\varphi$, and (twice) with the constant
$0$; \Cref{lem:stack}, which we re-verified here over all $192$ and all
$144$ pairs, gives the two conditions, and Holt--Klee is inherited by
faces.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The readout-free obstruction $(T^{\ast})$]\label{prop:tstar}
For an orientation $s$ of the $m$-cube and $z\in\{0,1\}^{m}$ let
\[
  D(s,z)\ :=\ \bigl(s(\cdot\oplus z)\bigr)\!*\!s\!*\!\mathbf 0
\]
be the orientation of the $(m+1)$-cube with $s(\cdot\oplus z)$ on the
facet $\{x_m=0\}$, $s$ on the facet $\{x_m=1\}$ and every edge in
direction $m$ pointing towards the facet $\{x_m=0\}$. If $B_\varphi(s,z)$
is Holt--Klee for even one readout $\varphi$, then $D(s,z)$ is Holt--Klee.
Restricted to $2$-faces this is exactly condition \emph{(T)} of
\Cref{lem:blowup-faces}; at higher dimensions it is strictly stronger.
Like \Cref{thm:deformed-flat}, this closes a construction avenue and is
not a barrier in the sense of \Cref{sec:gap}: it constrains one rule for
building orientations and covers none of the mechanisms M1--M6 or
\Cref{def:bsi}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By the table of \Cref{thm:blowup} the outer coordinate $\alpha$ lies in
$O_{10}$ for both readout values and in no $O_{00}$, so every
$\alpha$-edge between the layers $(1,0)$ and $(0,0)$ points towards
$(0,0)$, whatever $\varphi$ is; and the inner parts on those two layers
are $s$ and $s(\cdot\oplus z)$. Hence for every face $F$ of the $m$-cube
the face of $B_\varphi(s,z)$ on the directions of $F$ together with
$\alpha$, taken at $\beta=0$, is isomorphic to the corresponding face of
$D(s,z)$; Holt--Klee is inherited by faces. The $2$-face case is
\Cref{lem:stack}(b), which is \emph{(T)}. Strictness: for the seed
$s$ of \Cref{prop:hk-doubling-measured}(d) below at $m=5$ with $z=21$ all $80$
$2$-faces satisfy \emph{(T)} while $D(s,21)$ fails Holt--Klee already on a
$4$-face, and correspondingly no readout makes $B_\varphi(s,21)$
Holt--Klee: the failing faces are the two $4$-faces on
$\{x_0,x_3,x_4,\alpha\}$ and $\{x_0,x_3,x_4,\beta\}$ at the origin and the
$5$-face containing them, for every readout that satisfies
\emph{(P$_\varphi$)}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The blow-up doubles once, with a non-parity readout]\label{prop:hk-doubling-measured}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Over all $728$ acyclic unique sink orientations $s$ of the
$3$-cube, all $8$ translations $z$ and all $256$ readouts $\varphi$
--- $1\,490\,944$ orientations of the $5$-cube --- the greatest
bottom-antipodal height of a Holt--Klee $B_\varphi(s,z)$ is $8$: heights
$9$ and $10=2h^{\ast}(3)+2$ are attained, by $9216$ of the orientations,
but by no Holt--Klee one (the route, reproduced exhaustively by its
auditor).
\item[(b)] At $m=4$ it is attained. For the Holt--Klee seed
\[
  s=(0,1,3,2,6,13,12,7,14,9,8,11,10,5,4,15)
\]
of the $4$-cube, of height $6$ with sink $o=0$ and maximal vertices
$\{5,13\}$, and $z=o\oplus13=13$, the readout $\varphi$ with support
$\{1,8,9,10,12\}$ makes $B_\varphi(s,z)$ a Holt--Klee acyclic unique sink
orientation of the $6$-cube of height $14=2\cdot6+2=
2h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(4)+2$; it is the orientation $s_6$ of
\Cref{prop:hk-records}. Taking $\varphi$ to be the parity of the inner
height and flipping it at the \emph{other} maximal vertex $5$ also works.
So the obstruction of \Cref{cor:blowup-parity} is an obstruction to the
parity readout only.
\item[(c)] At $m=5$ it is attained again, at height $9$ rather than at
the ceiling $11$. For the Holt--Klee seed
\[
 s^{(5)}=(24,17,11,10,6,21,4,7,2,25,0,3,14,31,12,29,
          9,8,22,19,20,5,18,23,16,1,26,27,30,13,28,15)
\]
of the $5$-cube, of height $9$ with sink $o=10$, and $u=13$ (of height
$9$), $z=o\oplus u=7$, the readout $\varphi$ with support
\[
 \{0,2,3,10,12,14,16,17,18,19,22,24,26,27,28,30\}
\]
makes $B_\varphi(s^{(5)},7)$ a Holt--Klee acyclic unique sink orientation
of the $7$-cube of height $20=2\cdot9+2$; it is the orientation $s_7$ of
\Cref{prop:hk-records}. Three seeds pass $(T^{\ast})$ with $h(u)=9$ and
between them yield $96$ such readouts.
\item[(d)] The two conditions are of different strength, and only
$(T^{\ast})$ is predictive. Of the $785$ Holt--Klee classes of the
$4$-cube of height $5$ or $6$, exactly $506$ admit a $z=o\oplus u$
satisfying \emph{(T)} and $4$ admit one with $h(u)=6$; enumerating all
$2^{16}$ readouts over those four gives exactly $36$ Holt--Klee blow-ups
of height $14$. At $m=5$, of $85\,671$ Holt--Klee orientations produced by
the facet search, $6\,274$ pass \emph{(T)} for some $z=o\oplus u$, with
$h(u)$ up to $9$; the seed
\[
 (8,25,7,2,5,6,3,4,1,0,11,10,14,15,13,12,
  16,9,19,18,22,23,28,21,24,17,27,26,30,29,20,31)
\]
with $z=21$, $u=28$, $h(u)=8$ passes \emph{(T)} on all $80$ of its
$2$-faces, satisfies \emph{(P$_\varphi$)} for a readout, and yet admits
no Holt--Klee blow-up at all: $D(s,21)$ fails Holt--Klee on the $4$-face
$\{x_0,x_3,x_4,x_5\}$ at the origin, and every \emph{(P)}-feasible readout
leaves a defect of exactly three faces of $B_\varphi(s,21)$, the two
$4$-faces on $\{x_0,x_3,x_4,\alpha\}$ and $\{x_0,x_3,x_4,\beta\}$ at the
origin and the $5$-face containing them. Only $188$ of the $85\,671$ pass
$(T^{\ast})$ with $h(u)\ge6$, and just $3$ with $h(u)=9$.
\item[(e)] The chain stops there on our material. None of the $36$
height-$14$ orientations of the $6$-cube of (b), none of $502$ Holt--Klee
orientations of the $6$-cube of height $13$, and none of the $96$
height-$20$ orientations of the $7$-cube of (c) admits a translation
passing $(T^{\ast})$ with $h(u)\ge9$ (resp.\ $\ge14$); so none of them can
be blown up again, whatever the readout.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Exhaustive computations with the verified engine of
\Cref{prop:hk-records}; (a) is a complete scan of the stated space;
\emph{(T)} is tested on all $\binom m2 2^{m-2}$ $2$-faces and
$(T^{\ast})$ by one Holt--Klee test on the $(m+1)$-cube. In (b) and (c)
the readouts were checked from the definition of $B_\varphi$ and the
resulting orientations independently re-verified in a second
implementation.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[One player: $h^{\ast}_{1}(m+1)\ge h^{\ast}_{1}(m)+1$]\label{prop:oneplayer-plus-one}
Let $G$ be a stopping SSG with $\Vmin=\emptyset$, $|\Vmax|=m$, and an
all-switches run $\sigma_0,\dots,\sigma_L$ with $L\ge1$. Then there is a
stopping SSG $G'$ with $\Vmin=\emptyset$ and $|\Vmax|=m+1$ whose
all-switches run from an explicit start has length $L+1$; if $G$ is
nondegenerate then $G'$ can be taken nondegenerate. Hence
$h^{\ast}_{1}(m+1)\ge h^{\ast}_{1}(m)+1$. With
$h^{\ast}_{1}(6)\ge12$ of \Cref{prop:oneplayer-runs},
\[
  h^{\ast}_{1}(m)\ \ge\ m+6\qquad(m\ge6),
\]
so in particular $h^{\ast}_{1}(7)\ge13$, and since the witnesses are
nondegenerate one-player games, \Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp} gives
$h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{LP}}(m)\ge m+6$ for $m\ge6$ as well --- numerical
bounds along these witnesses, not a law for the LP ceiling: whether
$L(s)$ of \Cref{prop:sink-lift} is a linear-programming orientation
whenever $s$ is, is open. (Asymptotically \Cref{cor:stack-family}'s
$h^{\ast}_{1}(7k)\ge12k$ is stronger; the gain here is one row at a time
and every $m$.)
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Work in the harmonic normal form of \Cref{prop:oneplayer-runs}. The value
vectors of a run are pairwise distinct and nondecreasing
(\Cref{lem:switch}, \Cref{lem:flat-class}), so some $x_j\in\Vmax$ has
$\val_{\sigma_{L-1}}(x_j)<\val_{\sigma_L}(x_j)$. Fix
$\varepsilon:=2^{-D_0}$ with $D_0$ large enough that
$(1-\varepsilon)\val_{\sigma_{L-1}}(x_j)<(1-\varepsilon)\val_{\sigma_L}(x_j)$
still holds, and choose a dyadic $\theta$ strictly between them; by
\Cref{lem:denominator-sharp} two distinct values of $G$ differ by at least
$2^{-2a}$, so $\theta$ of denominator $2^{2a+2}$ exists. Adjoin one Max
vertex $y$ with the two rows
\[
  (y,0):\ p=0,\ q=\theta,\qquad (y,1):\ p=(1-\varepsilon)e_j,\ q=0,
\]
and leave every old row unchanged, with a zero entry in the new
coordinate; by \Cref{lem:dyadic-row} the two rows are realised by chains
of average vertices, and every row still leaks, so $G'$ is stopping. No
old vertex sees $y$, so $\val_\sigma$ on $\Vmax(G)$ and the switchable
sets of the old vertices are unchanged. Start at $\sigma_0$ with
$\sigma_0(y)=0$. For $t<L$ the alternative at $y$ has value
$(1-\varepsilon)\val_{\sigma_t}(x_j)\le
(1-\varepsilon)\val_{\sigma_{L-1}}(x_j)<\theta$, so $y$ is not switchable
and the run is that of $G$; at $\sigma_L$ the old switchable set is empty
while the alternative at $y$ has value
$(1-\varepsilon)\val_{\sigma_L}(x_j)>\theta$, so $S_{\sigma_L}=\{y\}$ and
the run continues one round. Nondegeneracy: $\theta$ ranges over an
interval of length at least $2^{-2a-1}$, so it may be chosen dyadic of
denominator $2^{K}$ with $K>2a+m+1$ to avoid the finitely many numbers
$(1-\varepsilon)\val_\sigma(x_j)$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{One player: Howard's rule with two actions, sign-definite games, and stacking}\label{sec:oneplayer-howard}

The one-player half of the pivot is Melekopoglou and Condon's question
(\Cref{rem:ladder}). The round-$16$ one-player route made the dictionary
of claim (v3) a proposition, isolated a class on which every improving
rule is linear, and found the first one-player family whose all-switches
run exceeds $|\Vmax|$ by a constant factor, by stacking copies of a block
behind a freezing bias.

\begin{remark}[The one-player dictionary]\label{rem:oneplayer-dictionary}
Claim (v3) of the earlier rounds --- that all-switches on a stopping
one-player SSG is Howard's policy-improvement rule on a transient
two-action Markov decision process, in both directions --- is settled by
\Cref{sec:readouts}: with $\Vmin=\emptyset$ every readout is a single
substochastic affine map (\Cref{lem:readout}(a)), the first-passage rows
onto $\Vmax\cup\{t_1\}$ are the transition rows and rewards of that
process, $\val_\sigma|_{\Vmax}$ is the value of the policy $\sigma$
(\Cref{lem:readout}(b)) and the strictly switchable set is the set of
improvable states (\Cref{lem:readout}(c)); conversely a transient
two-action process with dyadic rows of denominator $2^{d}$ is the process
of a stopping one-player SSG on $O(m^{2}d)$ average vertices
(\Cref{thm:readout-realise}(b) at $r=1$, \Cref{lem:dyadic-row}). So the
one-player ceiling $h^{\ast}_{1}$ is the worst case of Howard's rule with
two actions per state, Melekopoglou and Condon's question.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[Sign-definite games]\label{def:signdef}
Let $G$ be a stopping SSG and, for $v\in V$, let $p^{v}$ be the law of the first visit
to $C\cup\{t_0,t_1\}$ from $v$. Call $G$ \emph{sign-definite} if for every
$v\in\Vmax$ the vector $d^{v}:=\bigl(p^{v^{(1)}}-p^{v^{(0)}}\bigr)\big|_{C}$ is
sign-definite: either $d^{v}\ge0$ or $d^{v}\le0$ componentwise.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[All-switches is linear on sign-definite games]\label{thm:signdef}
Let $G$ be a sign-definite stopping SSG, and let $\sigma_0,\sigma_1,\dots$ be any run
of improving switches (all-switches included). Then every $v\in\Vmax$ switches at most
twice, so the run has length at most $2|\Vmax|$; membership is decidable in
polynomial time. (Every improving rule is therefore polynomial on the class;
whether the class contains a member outside the seven polynomial classes
of this document and the quasipolynomial class of \Cref{thm:qp} is not
determined --- the random samples below all have
$\min(|\Vmax|,|\Vmin|)\le2$ --- so it is recorded as a bound on the rule and
not as a new polynomial class.)
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
As in \Cref{rem:oneplayer-dictionary}, for every Max strategy $\rho$ and every $v$,
$\val_\rho(v)=\sum_{w\in C}p^{v}_w\val_\rho(w)+p^{v}(t_1)$, this time with $C$ in
place of $\Vmax$ and $\val_\rho$ the value against a Min best response. Hence
\[
  \phi_v(t)\;:=\;\val_{\sigma_t}(v^{(1)})-\val_{\sigma_t}(v^{(0)})
  \;=\;\sum_{w\in C}d^{v}_w\,\val_{\sigma_t}(w)+e_v ,
\]
with $e_v$ independent of $t$. By \Cref{lem:switch} the vector $\val_{\sigma_t}$ is
nondecreasing in $t$, so if $d^{v}\ge0$ then $\phi_v$ is nondecreasing, and if
$d^{v}\le0$ it is nonincreasing. A vertex switches at $t$ exactly when $\phi_v(t)$ is
nonzero and its sign disagrees with the current choice; after such a switch the choice
agrees with the sign of $\phi_v$, and a monotone real sequence changes sign at most
once, so at most one further switch can occur. Hence $n_v\le2$ and, since each round
switches at least one vertex, $L\le2|\Vmax|$. The run ends at an optimal strategy
(\Cref{lem:local-global}), each round costs one Min best response and one evaluation
(\Cref{thm:eval-stopfree}), and each $p^{v}$ is one rational linear solve on the
average vertices, so both the algorithm and the membership test are polynomial.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:signdef}
The class admits two players, cycles and $a=\Theta(N)$. It is \emph{not}
closed under \Cref{def:damping}: the chain of \Cref{lem:gadget} multiplies
the weight of a path by $1-2^{-m}$ per edge, so the two first-passage laws
at a Max vertex are reweighted entry by entry according to the depths of
their targets, not by one common scalar. On the stopping game with
$\Vmax=\{v,w_1,w_2\}$, $v\to(B,A)$, $w_1\to(t_1,t_0)$, $w_2\to(t_0,t_1)$,
$A\to(a_1,w_2)$, $a_1\to(w_1,w_1)$, $B\to(w_1,b_1)$, $b_1\to(w_2,t_0)$
($9$ vertices) one has $d^{v}=(0,0,\tfrac14)$ on $(v,w_1,w_2)$, while in
$G_m$ the same vector is
$\bigl(0,\,-\lambda^{2}(1-\lambda)/2,\,\lambda^{2}(2-\lambda)/4\bigr)$ with
$\lambda=1-2^{-m}$, i.e.\ $(0,-\tfrac1{16},\tfrac3{32})$,
$(0,-\tfrac9{128},\tfrac{45}{256})$, $(0,-\tfrac{49}{1024},\tfrac{441}{2048})$
at $m=1,2,3$: both signs present (the round-$17$ paper audit, recomputed
here from the definitions, \texttt{scripts/round17-verify/signdef\_damping.py}).
Sign-definiteness is therefore a property of the stopping game as
presented, and the reduction of \Cref{thm:stopping-transform} need not
preserve it. The content of \Cref{thm:signdef} is the contrapositive,
which is a design constraint on every attempt at a long run --- \emph{a run longer than
$2|\Vmax|$ needs a Max vertex whose two successors have first-passage laws whose
difference changes sign on $C$}, i.e.\ a vertex that genuinely trades one controlled
vertex off against another rather than trading a bundle against a constant. The
ladder $L_n$ of \Cref{def:ladder} is not sign-definite; the chain $\mathrm{RC}(k)$ of
\Cref{rem:grid-per-vertex} is. In two independent samples of random stopping SSGs
($882$ on $6$--$10$ non-sink vertices, of which $668$ are sign-definite, and $1009$ on
$8$--$13$, of which $445$ are) the longest run inside the class was $3$ and the
maximum number of switches at a single vertex was exactly $2$: the bound $n_v\le2$ is
attained.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[Balanced normal form]\label{def:balanced}
A one-player normal form on states $B$, given by substochastic rows $p^{b,c}$ over
$B$ and rewards $q_{b,c}\ge0$, is \emph{balanced with mass $\rho$} if
$\sum_{b'}p^{b,c}_{b'}=\rho<1$ for every $b\in B$ and $c\in\{0,1\}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Balancing costs one state]\label{lem:balance}
Let $G_0$ be a nondegenerate one-player normal form on $m_0$ states with
$\rho:=\max_{b,c}\sum_{b'}p^{b,c}_{b'}<1$, all-switches run of length $h_0$ from
$\sigma^{0}$, and gaps
$g_{\min}:=\min_{\sigma,b}\bigl|A_{b,1}(y_\sigma)-A_{b,0}(y_\sigma)\bigr|>0$,
$g_{\max}$ the corresponding maximum. For every $\mu>0$ with
$\rho+\mu\max q_{b,c}<1$ and every dyadic $\varsigma$ with
$0<\varsigma<(1-\rho)^{2}\mu g_{\min}/4$ there is a \emph{balanced} nondegenerate
one-player normal form $\widehat G_0(\mu,\varsigma)$ on $m_0+1$ states with mass
$\rho$, namely: scale every reward by $\mu$; add a state $\omega$ with
$p^{\omega,c}_\omega=\rho$ and rewards $0,\varsigma$; and pad the row of $(b,c)$ with
$\rho-\sum_{b'}p^{b,c}_{b'}$ on $\omega$. Its values are
$\mu\,y_\sigma+\pi_{\sigma}\varsigma/(1-\rho)$ on the old states with
$\pi_\sigma\in[0,1]$ the pad of the chosen row, $\omega$ is never strictly switchable
at its action $1$, and the all-switches run from $(\sigma^{0},1)$ is the run of $G_0$,
of length $h_0$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Scaling every reward by $\mu$ scales every value by $\mu$ and every action value by
$\mu$, so it changes no comparison. The added state satisfies
$x_\omega=\rho x_\omega+r_c$, i.e.\ $x_\omega=r_c/(1-\rho)$, so its action $1$ is
strictly better and it never switches from there; and the pad injects at
most $\varsigma/(1-\rho)$ at every state, which propagates through
$(I-P_\sigma)^{-1}$, of row sums at most $1/(1-\rho)$, so it perturbs each
value by at most $\varsigma/(1-\rho)^{2}$ and each comparison by at most
$2\varsigma/(1-\rho)^{2}<\mu g_{\min}/2$, less than half the smallest gap
(the route's bound omitted one factor $1/(1-\rho)$; the built family uses
$\varsigma=2^{-60}\mu g_{\min}$, far inside either). Hence every comparison keeps its sign and the improvement orientation on the old
coordinates is unchanged.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Stacking: the run adds up]\label{thm:stack}
Let $\widehat G$ be a balanced nondegenerate one-player normal form on $m_0$ states
with mass $\rho$, gaps in $[g_{\min},g_{\max}]$, and an all-switches run of length
$h_0$ from $\sigma^{0}$. For $k\ge1$ let $\mathrm{ST}(k)$ be the one-player normal form
on $k\,m_0$ states built from $k$ disjoint copies $B_1,\dots,B_k$ of $\widehat G$ by
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em]
\item scaling the rewards of copy $j$ by $\mu_j>0$;
\item giving every state $b$ of copy $j\ge2$ the extra mass $\nu$ on a constant
      gadget of value $\Theta_j$ on the action $\sigma^{0}(b)$, and the extra mass
      $\nu$ on the state $v_{j-1}$ of copy $j-1$ on the other action, where $v_{j-1}$
      is a state that copy $j-1$ switches at the last round of its run --- the
      within-copy rows being re-padded so that the within-copy mass stays $\rho$ for
      both actions.
\end{itemize}
Let $x^{-}_j<x^{+}_j$ be the values of $v_{j-1}$ in $\mathrm{ST}(j-1)$ at the last two
strategies of its run, put $\Gamma_j:=x^{+}_j-x^{-}_j>0$, and choose
\[
  0<\epsilon<\frac{(1-\rho)g_{\min}}{(1-\rho)g_{\min}+g_{\max}},\qquad
  \Theta_j:=x^{+}_j-\epsilon\Gamma_j,\qquad
  \frac{\nu\epsilon\Gamma_j}{(1-\rho)g_{\min}}<\mu_j<\frac{\nu(1-\epsilon)\Gamma_j}{g_{\max}} .
\]
Then the all-switches run of $\mathrm{ST}(k)$ from the strategy that puts every copy at
$\sigma^{0}$ has length exactly $k\,h_0$: copy $j$ is frozen at $\sigma^{0}$ for the
first $(j-1)h_0$ rounds and performs $\widehat G$'s run during rounds
$(j-1)h_0,\dots,jh_0-1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Copies read only themselves and the single state $v_{j-1}$ of the previous copy, so
the transition matrix is block lower triangular and the values of copies
$1,\dots,j$ do not depend on copies $>j$; in particular $\mathrm{ST}(j-1)$'s values are
the restriction of $\mathrm{ST}(k)$'s, and the recursion defining $\Theta_j,\mu_j$ is
well founded.

Fix $j$ and a Max strategy $\sigma$ of $\mathrm{ST}(k)$, and write $x$ for the value
vector, $P_\sigma$ for the within-copy transition matrix of copy $j$ and
$y_\sigma$ for the value of $\sigma|_{B_j}$ in $\widehat G$. The equations of copy $j$
read $x_b=(P_\sigma x)_b+\mu_jq_{b,\sigma(b)}+\beta_b$ with
$\beta_b=\nu\Theta_j$ if $\sigma(b)=\sigma^{0}(b)$ and $\beta_b=\nu x_{v_{j-1}}$
otherwise. With $\delta:=x_{v_{j-1}}-\Theta_j$ and
$\chi_\sigma(b):=\mathbf 1[\sigma(b)\ne\sigma^{0}(b)]$ this is
$\beta=\nu\Theta_j\mathbf 1+\nu\delta\chi_\sigma$, and since every row of $P_\sigma$
has mass exactly $\rho$ we have $(I-P_\sigma)^{-1}\mathbf 1=(1-\rho)^{-1}\mathbf 1$,
whence
\[
  x|_{B_j}\;=\;\mu_j\,y_\sigma\;+\;\frac{\nu\Theta_j}{1-\rho}\mathbf 1
  \;+\;\nu\delta\,u_\sigma,\qquad u_\sigma:=(I-P_\sigma)^{-1}\chi_\sigma\in\Bigl[0,\tfrac1{1-\rho}\Bigr]^{B_j}.
\]
Because both rows at $b$ have the same mass $\rho$, $(p^{b,1}-p^{b,0})\cdot\mathbf 1=0$
and $(p^{b,1}-p^{b,0})\cdot u\le\rho\,\mathrm{osc}(u)$; so the comparison
$C_b:=(\text{value of action }1)-(\text{value of action }0)$ at $b$ is
\[
  C_b\;=\;\mu_j\,\Delta_b(y_\sigma)\;-\;s_b\,\nu\delta\;+\;\nu\delta\,\eta_b,
  \qquad |\eta_b|\le\frac{\rho}{1-\rho},
\]
where $\Delta_b(y):=A_{b,1}(y)-A_{b,0}(y)$ is $\widehat G$'s own difference and
$s_b=+1$ if $\sigma^{0}(b)=1$, $s_b=-1$ if $\sigma^{0}(b)=0$.

\emph{Freezing.} Suppose $\sigma|_{B_j}=\sigma^{0}$ and $x_{v_{j-1}}\le x^{-}_j$. Then
$\chi_\sigma=0$, so $\eta_b=0$ \emph{exactly}, and
$\delta\le x^{-}_j-\Theta_j=-(1-\epsilon)\Gamma_j<0$. Hence
$C_b=\mu_j\Delta_b(y_{\sigma^{0}})-s_b\nu\delta$ has the sign of $-s_b\nu\delta$,
i.e.\ the sign that keeps $\sigma^{0}(b)$, as soon as
$\nu(1-\epsilon)\Gamma_j>\mu_jg_{\max}$ --- the right-hand inequality of the display in
the statement. So no state of copy $j$ is strictly switchable.

\emph{Running.} Suppose $x_{v_{j-1}}=x^{+}_j$, so $\delta=\epsilon\Gamma_j$. Then
$|C_b-\mu_j\Delta_b(y_\sigma)|\le\nu\epsilon\Gamma_j(1+\rho/(1-\rho))
=\nu\epsilon\Gamma_j/(1-\rho)<\mu_jg_{\min}\le\mu_j|\Delta_b(y_\sigma)|$ by the
left-hand inequality; so $C_b$ and $\Delta_b(y_\sigma)$ have the same sign for every
$\sigma$ and every $b$. The improvement orientation of copy $j$ is therefore
$\widehat G$'s, and its all-switches trajectory from $\sigma^{0}$ is $\widehat G$'s run.

\emph{The induction.} By induction on $j$, copy $j$ is at $\sigma^{0}$ and frozen while
$x_{v_{j-1}}\le x^{-}_j$, which by \Cref{lem:switch} (values are nondecreasing along the
run, and $v_{j-1}$ takes the value $x^{-}_j$ at round $(j-1)h_0-1$ and $x^{+}_j$ at
round $(j-1)h_0$) is exactly the first $(j-1)h_0$ rounds; from round $(j-1)h_0$ on,
$x_{v_{j-1}}=x^{+}_j$ is constant, because copies $\le j-1$ are unaffected by copies
$\ge j$ and copy $j-1$ has reached its optimum. Copy $j$ then runs for $h_0$ rounds.
The copies therefore run one after another and the total is $k\,h_0$. Finally
$\epsilon<(1-\rho)g_{\min}/((1-\rho)g_{\min}+g_{\max})$ is exactly the condition that
the interval for $\mu_j$ is nonempty.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[A one-player family with slope $12/7$]\label{cor:stack-family}
Let $G_0$ be the $m=6$ one-player normal form behind the entry
$h^{\ast}_{1}(6)\ge12$ of \Cref{rem:four-ceilings} (round-$15$ howard-cube route; its
rows have denominator $2^{12}$ and its all-switches run from $\sigma=25$ has length
$12$), and let $\widehat G:=\widehat G_0(\mu,\varsigma)$ be its balanced form on $7$
states (\Cref{lem:balance}). Then for every $k$ there is a one-player
stopping SSG with $7k$ Max vertices, dyadic data of $O(k)$ bits and $O(k^{3})$ average
vertices, whose all-switches run from the stated start has length $12k$;
it is nondegenerate for $k\le2$ (exhaustive over all incidences, by the
route's auditor), and \Cref{thm:stack} excludes ties only along the
designated run, so nondegeneracy for every $k$ is not proved. Hence
\[
  h^{\ast}_{1}(m)\;\ge\;\tfrac{12}{7}\,m\qquad\text{for } m\equiv0 \pmod 7 ,
\]
and more generally $h^{\ast}_{1}(m_1+k(m_0+1))\ge h_1+k\,h_0$ whenever a one-player
normal form on $m_1$ states has a run of length $h_1$ and a
\emph{nondegenerate} one on $m_0$ states has a run of length $h_0$ ---
\Cref{lem:balance,thm:stack} need the gap $g_{\min}>0$ of the block that
is balanced and stacked, while copy $1$, which nothing perturbs, may be
degenerate. Writing $h^{\ast,\mathrm{nd}}_{1}$ for the ceiling over
nondegenerate one-player games, this gives
$h^{\ast}_{1}(m_1+m_2+1)\ge h^{\ast}_{1}(m_1)+h^{\ast,\mathrm{nd}}_{1}(m_2)$
and $\liminf_m h^{\ast}_{1}(m)/m\ge\sup_{m_0}h^{\ast,\mathrm{nd}}_{1}(m_0)/(m_0+1)$.
Superadditivity of $h^{\ast}_{1}$ itself --- which by Fekete's lemma would
make $\lim_m h^{\ast}_{1}(m)/m$ exist --- is \emph{not} established: the
stacked game need not be nondegenerate, \Cref{thm:stack} excluding ties
only along the designated run (the round-$17$ paper audit, correcting an
earlier claim here). What the construction does show is its own cap: the
run of a stack is the sum of its blocks' runs, so its length per state
never exceeds that of its best block, and stacking can never by itself
make $h^{\ast}_{1}$ superlinear. And since
$\mathrm{ST}(2)$ is nondegenerate, \Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp} makes its
orientation Holt--Klee, so $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(14)\ge h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{LP}}(14)\ge24$
--- weaker than the $27$ that the sink lift of \Cref{prop:sink-lift} gives
from $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(7)\ge20$, but obtained where
\Cref{lem:hstar-super}'s abstract product leaves the Holt--Klee class
(\Cref{rem:four-ceilings}); the constructions add heights by different
means, the product by a conditional translation, the lift by one
coordinate, and the stack by a freezing bias that keeps every copy alive.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
\Cref{lem:balance} and \Cref{thm:stack}; the parameters are
$\rho=4095/4096$, $\nu=2^{-20}$, $\mu_1=2^{-12}$,
$\epsilon=g_{\min}/(2^{24}g_{\max})$, $\mu_{j+1}=2^{-31}\mu_j$ and $\Theta_{j+1}$ a
dyadic number in $[x^{+}_{j+1}-\epsilon\Gamma_{j+1},\,x^{+}_{j+1}-\tfrac12\epsilon\Gamma_{j+1}]$
--- rounded \emph{up}, since the freezing inequality of \Cref{thm:stack} is
tight at $x^{+}_{j+1}-\epsilon\Gamma_{j+1}$ and the interval for $\mu_j$ has
ratio $2$, which absorbs the rounding (the route's auditor); each $\Theta_j$ and $\mu_j$
needs $O(j)$ bits, so \Cref{lem:dyadic-row} gives $O((7k)^2\cdot O(k))=O(k^{3})$
average vertices. For the last clause take copy $1$ to be the unpadded first game (no
bias is attached to copy $1$, so it needs no balancing) and copies $2,\dots$ to be
balanced copies of the second: the balance hypothesis of \Cref{thm:stack} is used
only for the copies $j\ge2$, which read one state of the previous copy, so the
proof goes through unchanged (rebuilt and confirmed at $13,20,27$ states with
runs $24,36,48$ by the route's auditor).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What was computed]\label{rem:stack-measured}
The family of \Cref{cor:stack-family} was built and its run verified in exact rational
arithmetic for $k=1,\dots,10$, giving
$(m,L)=(7,12),(14,24),(21,36),(28,48),(35,60),(42,72),(49,84),(56,96),(63,108),(70,120)$;
the value vector increases pointwise at every round of every run. The raw SSGs were
also built through \Cref{lem:dyadic-row} and the runs recomputed \emph{from the games}
by a second, independent evaluator: $\mathrm{ST}(1)$ is a stopping one-player SSG on
$N=734$ vertices ($7$ Max) with a run of length $12$, and $\mathrm{ST}(2)$ one on
$N=2199$ vertices ($14$ Max) with a run of length $24$ ($\mathrm{ST}(1)$
re-verified here from its game file,
\texttt{scripts/round16-verify/ST1\_GAME.json}: no tied incidence, a
unique sink orientation, acyclic, height $12$; $\mathrm{ST}(2)$'s game file
is not recorded here, and its run of $24$ was recomputed through its exact
first-passage normal form over all $2^{14}$ starts,
\texttt{scripts/round16-verify/st2\_nf.py}). The construction is the first
family of one-player stopping SSGs whose all-switches run exceeds $|\Vmax|$ by a
constant factor: the previous record families, $\mathrm{RC}(k)$ and the ladder, both
have $L=|\Vmax|$, and \Cref{cor:gate}'s attained example has $L=|\Vmax|+2$.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[$h^{\ast}_{1}(5)\ge10$]\label{prop:hstar-one-five}
Freezing the fifth Max vertex of the $m=6$ one-player game of \Cref{cor:stack-family} at its
first action and retyping it as an average vertex (\Cref{def:subcube}) gives a
nondegenerate one-player stopping SSG on $346$ vertices with $5$ Max vertices whose
all-switches run from the strategy $01010$ has length $10$, with switched sets
\[
 \{0,1,3,4\},\{0,1,2,4\},\{0,1,4\},\{0,1,3\},\{0,1\},\{0\},\{2,3,4\},\{3,4\},\{4\},\{2\}.
\]
Its improvement orientation is an acyclic Holt--Klee unique sink orientation of the
$5$-cube of bottom-antipodal height $10$. Hence $h^{\ast}_{1}(5)\ge10$ and
$h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{LP}}(5)\ge10$, improving the entries $\ge9$ of
\Cref{rem:four-ceilings}; the Holt--Klee ceiling $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}(5)=11$ of
\Cref{prop:hkfive} is now within one.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A finite exact computation, done twice: once on the normal form obtained by
eliminating the frozen state (stochastic complementation), and once on the explicit
SSG, by an evaluator that resolves the average part of the graph in topological order
and solves the resulting $6\times6$ rational system. All $32$ starting strategies were
tried; $10$ is the maximum, and the other eleven facets of the $m=6$ record give
$5,6,6,6,6,6,6,7,8,8,6$. The orientation was checked to be a USO, acyclic, tie-free
and Holt--Klee (the max-flow test of \Cref{prop:hkfive}).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[$h^{\ast}_{1}(8)\ge13$]\label{prop:hstar-one-eight}
A one-player normal form on $8$ states with denominator $2^{44}$,
found by annealing and verified exactly (tie-free, USO, acyclic, Holt--Klee),
has bottom-antipodal height $13$: its run from $\sigma=10011000$ switches
\[
\{0,1,2,4,6,7\},\{0,1,2,3,5\},\{0,1,2,5\},\{0,1,5\},\{0,1,3\},\{0,1\},\{0\},
\{2,3,4,5,6\},\{3,5\},\{5\},\{2\},\{4\},\{6\}.
\]
So $h^{\ast}_{1}(8)\ge13$ and $h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{LP}}(8)\ge13$ (the game,
on $2691$ vertices, was built and its run recomputed from the graph by
the route's auditor). With
\Cref{prop:hstar-one-five} the one-player row of \Cref{rem:four-ceilings} becomes
\[
  h^{\ast}_{1}(m)\;=\;1,\;2,\;4,\;6,\;\ge10,\;\ge12,\;\ge12,\;\ge13\qquad(m=1,\dots,8),
\]
and \Cref{cor:stack-family} adds $h^{\ast}_{1}(7k)\ge12k$ for every $k$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}[Exhaustive census at $N\le8$]\label{prop:oneplayer-census-small}
Over \emph{all} one-player stopping SSGs whose non-sink vertices number
$n\le 6$ --- all $\binom{n+2}{2}^{\,n}$ assignments of an unordered pair of
\emph{distinct} targets to each vertex, for every split of $n$ into $|\Vmax|=m$ and
$a=n-m$; a vertex whose two successors coincide may be contracted, which lowers $n$
and does not raise $m$, so this is no loss --- the longest all-switches run over all
$2^{m}$ starting strategies is exactly $m$, for every $(m,a)$ with $3\le m\le n$
(for $m\le2$ the same bound is the known $h^{\ast}_{1}(1)=1$, $h^{\ast}_{1}(2)=2$, and
for $a=0$ it is \Cref{rem:grid-per-vertex}). In particular $L\le N-2$ on all of them,
with equality only at $a=0$, where the chain $\mathrm{RC}(m)$ attains it; average
vertices never lengthen a run at these sizes, so the smallest one-player game with
$L>|\Vmax|$ --- one exists, since $h^{\ast}_{1}(3)=4$ --- has at least $7$ non-sink
vertices. The computation is exact (integer Bareiss elimination, values compared as
numerators over the common determinant) and covers $1.5\times10^{9}$ games.
\end{proposition}

\subsection{Readouts: what a game presents to its Max vertices}\label{sec:readouts}

The searches behind \Cref{prop:auso-seven,prop:b2-realised} and the
one-player census of \Cref{rem:auso-census} all parametrise a game by
first-passage rows and read the orientation off them. The round-$15$
monotone route made that practice a theorem, and it separates the two
players cleanly: one player buys affine readouts, the second buys
concavity, and nothing else.

\begin{definition}[Pinning, and the readouts]\label{def:readout}
Let $G$ be stopping with $\Vmax=\{x_1,\dots,x_m\}$ and let
$y\in[0,1]^{m}$ be rational. Let $G[y]$ be the payoff game
(\Cref{def:payoff}, \Cref{rem:terminals}) obtained from $G$ by retyping
every Max vertex $x_v$ as a terminal of payoff $y_v$; it is stopping with
no Max vertices, and for dyadic $y$ it is also realised as an SSG by
replacing each such terminal by a chain of \Cref{lem:dyadic-row} of
value $y_v$. For $v\in\Vmax$
and $a\in\{0,1\}$ with successor $v^{(a)}$ put
$F_{v,a}(y):=\val_{G[y]}(v^{(a)})$, the \emph{readouts} of $G$. An affine
map $y\mapsto q+p\cdot y$ on $[0,1]^{m}$ is \emph{substochastic} when
$p,q\ge0$ and $|p|_1+q\le1$: exactly the expected payoffs of first-passage
laws landing on the $x_w$ with probabilities $p_w$, on $t_1$ with
probability $q$ and on $t_0$ with the rest.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[The readout theorem]\label{lem:readout}
Let $G$ be stopping with $|\Vmax|=m$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item For rational $y$,
      $F_{v,a}(y)=\min_{\tau}(q^{v,a,\tau}+p^{v,a,\tau}\cdot y)$ over the
      positional Min strategies $\tau$ of $G$, where
      $(p^{v,a,\tau},q^{v,a,\tau})$ is the first-passage law from
      $v^{(a)}$ onto $\Vmax$ and $t_1$ when Min plays $\tau$; the formula
      extends $F_{v,a}$ to $[0,1]^{m}$. Each piece is substochastic, so
      every $F_{v,a}$ is nondecreasing, concave and $1$-Lipschitz for the
      sup norm, and a single substochastic affine map when
      $\Vmin=\emptyset$.
\item For every Max strategy $\sigma$ the restriction
      $x_\sigma:=\val_\sigma|_{\Vmax}$ --- rational, and not dyadic in
      general, its denominators dividing integers at most $2^{a}$
      (\Cref{lem:denominator-sharp}) --- is the unique $y\in[0,1]^{m}$ with
      $y_v=F_{v,\sigma(v)}(y)$ for all $v$, and $\val_\sigma=\val_{G[x_\sigma]}$
      off $\Vmax$.
\item $v\in S_\sigma$ iff $F_{v,1-\sigma(v)}(x_\sigma)>x_\sigma(v)$, and
      the incidence $(\sigma,v)$ is tied iff equality holds.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) A trap of $G[y]$ contains no chain vertex, every chain leaking to
a sink, so it avoids the former Max vertices and is a trap of $G$, hence
empty (\Cref{lem:trapchar}). $G[y]$ has no Max vertices, so its value is
the minimum over Min strategies of the frozen chain values, attained by a
positional one (\Cref{thm:stopping-determinacy}); the chain frozen at
$\tau$ absorbs almost surely in the chains and the sinks, the chain of
$x_w$ being worth $y_w$, so its value at $v^{(a)}$ is
$q^{v,a,\tau}+p^{v,a,\tau}\cdot y$. A minimum of finitely many
nondecreasing substochastic affine maps is nondecreasing, concave and
$1$-Lipschitz. (b) Extend $\val_\sigma$ to $G[x_\sigma]$ by the chain
values. Off the chains the Shapley operator of $G[x_\sigma]$ and the
frozen operator $T_\sigma$ agree, and on the chains both return the
chain values, the entry of the chain of $x_v$ being worth
$x_\sigma(v)=\val_\sigma(\sigma(v))$; so the extension is a fixed point of
the operator of the stopping game $G[x_\sigma]$, unique by
\Cref{thm:contraction}, whence $\val_{G[x_\sigma]}=\val_\sigma$ off the
chains and $F_{v,\sigma(v)}(x_\sigma)=\val_\sigma(v^{(\sigma(v))})=x_\sigma(v)$.
Uniqueness: $y\mapsto(F_{v,\sigma(v)}(y))_v$ is a minimum of substochastic
affine maps whose pieces are first-passage laws of positional pairs of
the stopping game $G$, so by the argument of \Cref{lem:survival-contract}
--- a piece of full mass supported on a set $U$ would put a positional
pair's token in a trap --- some power of it is a sup-norm contraction,
and its fixed point is unique. (c) By (b),
$\val_\sigma(v^{(b)})=F_{v,b}(x_\sigma)$ for both $b$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Eliminating a vertex's own value]\label{lem:readout-reduce}
Every piece of $F_{v,a}$ has $p_v<1$, so $t=F_{v,a}(y_{-v},t)$ has a
unique solution $\Psi_{v,a}(y_{-v})$, and
$\Psi_{v,a}=\min_\tau\Psi^{\tau}_{v,a}$ with
$\Psi^{\tau}_{v,a}(y_{-v})=(q+p_{-v}\cdot y_{-v})/(1-p_v)$ again
substochastic affine in $m-1$ variables. Moreover
$x_\sigma(v)=\Psi_{v,\sigma(v)}(x_{\sigma,-v})$, and $v\in S_\sigma$ iff
$\Psi_{v,1-\sigma(v)}(x_{\sigma,-v})>\Psi_{v,\sigma(v)}(x_{\sigma,-v})$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
A piece with $p_v=1$ would return the token from $v$ to $v$ almost surely
under some pair, against stopping; so $t\mapsto F_{v,a}(y_{-v},t)-t$ is
strictly decreasing with a unique zero. Solving each piece gives the
displayed map, whose coefficients are nonnegative with
$(|p_{-v}|_1+q)/(1-p_v)\le1$; the minimum passes to the solutions because
each piece dominates $F_{v,a}$ and the piece attaining the minimum at the
zero has the same zero. The identities are \Cref{lem:readout}(b),(c) read
at $t=x_\sigma(v)$, using that $t\mapsto F_{v,1-\sigma(v)}(y_{-v},t)-t$
is decreasing.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Readout system]\label{def:readout-system}
A \emph{readout system} of order $(m,r)$ is a family
$\Psi_{v,a}:[0,1]^{m-1}\to[0,1]$, $v\le m$, $a\in\{0,1\}$, each a minimum
of at most $r$ substochastic affine maps; it is \emph{$\delta$-leaky} if
every piece has $|p|_1+q\le1-\delta$ and \emph{dyadic} if all its
coefficients are. For $\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{m}$ let $x_\sigma$ be a fixed
point of $y\mapsto(\Psi_{v,\sigma(v)}(y_{-v}))_v$, unique when
$\delta>0$. The system \emph{realises} the outmap $s$ if for all
$\sigma,v$ the numbers $\Psi_{v,1-\sigma(v)}(x_{\sigma,-v})$ and
$x_\sigma(v)$ differ, the former being larger exactly when $v\in s(\sigma)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Realisability is a concave interpolation problem]\label{thm:readout-realise}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item If $s$ is the improvement outmap of a nondegenerate stopping SSG
      with $m$ Max and $k$ Min vertices, the family of
      \Cref{lem:readout-reduce} is a rational readout system of order
      $(m,2^{k})$ realising $s$, affine ($r=1$) when $k=0$.
\item If a dyadic $\delta$-leaky readout system of order $(m,r)$ and
      denominator $2^{D}$ with $\delta>0$ realises $s$, then $s$ is the
      improvement outmap of a nondegenerate stopping SSG with exactly
      $m$ Max vertices, at most $2m(r-1)$ Min vertices and at most
      $2mr(m+1)D$ average vertices.
\end{enumerate}
The two halves do not close to a size-faithful equivalence: (a) delivers
$r\le2^{k}$ pieces while (b) spends $r-1$ Min vertices per readout, and
(a) delivers rational pieces that need not leak, while (b) needs dyadic
leaking ones; whether every rational readout system realising a
nondegenerate $s$ can be replaced by a dyadic $\delta$-leaky one
realising the same $s$ was shown by the route for one player only, by
perturbation, and is left open here for two.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(a) is \Cref{lem:readout,lem:readout-reduce}, the pieces being indexed
by positional Min strategies. (b) For each $(v,a)$ chain $r-1$ Min
vertices $u^{v,a}_1,\dots,u^{v,a}_{r-1}$, action $0$ of $u^{v,a}_j$
entering the gadget of the $j$-th piece and action $1$ entering
$u^{v,a}_{j+1}$, the last entering the $r$-th gadget, and let action $a$
of $x_v$ point at $u^{v,a}_1$; each piece is a dyadic row over the $m$
Max vertices and $t_1$ leaking at least $\delta$ to $t_0$, realised by
\Cref{lem:dyadic-row} on at most $(m+1)D$ average vertices. Every gadget
leaks, so the operator of every positional pair is a contraction and the
only trap is empty; the readouts of the game are the given $\Psi_{v,a}$,
Min choosing the least piece, so by \Cref{lem:readout-reduce} the value
restrictions are the $x_\sigma$ and the outmap is $s$, nondegenerate by
the strict inequalities.
\end{proof}

For a fixed configuration $(x_\sigma)_\sigma$ the existence of the
readouts is a linear programme: a concave function interpolating finite
data exists iff each datum carries a supporting affine map dominating the
others, so the unknowns are, per $(v,a)$, a value $g_\sigma$ (pinned to
$x_\sigma(v)$ where $\sigma(v)=a$, free otherwise) and a gradient
$p_\sigma$ per point, with
$g_\sigma+p_\sigma\cdot(x_{\sigma',-v}-x_{\sigma,-v})\ge g_{\sigma'}$ and
the substochastic bounds; the one-player case ties all $p_\sigma$, and
\Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp} is the statement that the tied programme forces
Holt--Klee.

\begin{theorem}[Min vertices are needed in proportion to the Holt--Klee defect]\label{thm:min-count}
For an outmap $s$ of the $m$-cube let $\chi_{\mathrm{HK}}(s)$ be the
least $R$ such that Holt--Klee acyclic unique sink orientations
$t_1,\dots,t_R$ exist with $s(\sigma)\in\{t_1(\sigma),\dots,t_R(\sigma)\}$
for every $\sigma$. If $s$ is the improvement outmap of a nondegenerate
stopping SSG with $m$ Max and $k$ Min vertices then
$\chi_{\mathrm{HK}}(s)\le2^{k}$, so $k\ge\log_2\chi_{\mathrm{HK}}(s)$;
the same holds on every face. In particular a non-Holt--Klee $s$ needs
$k\ge1$, and if one Min strategy is a best response to every $\sigma$
then $s$ is Holt--Klee. Trivially $\chi_{\mathrm{HK}}(s)\le2^{m}$ for
every outmap $s$ --- the orientation with every edge pointing towards
$\sigma\oplus s(\sigma)$ is a linear-programming orientation, hence
Holt--Klee, and agrees with $s$ at $\sigma$ --- so the bound never asks
for more than $m$ Min vertices. The bound is attained at $B^{2}$, where
$\chi_{\mathrm{HK}}=2$ and \Cref{prop:b2-realised} has $k=1$, and at
$m=3$, where \Cref{prop:m3-realised} first used six Min vertices,
\Cref{prop:m3-one-min} attains it with one.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Choose a best response $\tau_\sigma$ for each $\sigma$ and let $T$ be
the set of responses chosen, $|T|\le2^{k}$. For $\tau\in T$ the game
$G_\tau$ with Min frozen at $\tau$ is one-player and stopping --- freezing
creates no trap, \Cref{lem:trapchar} --- with $\val^{G_\tau}_\sigma=\val_\sigma$ at least on
$S_\tau:=\{\sigma:\tau_\sigma=\tau\}$; there its incidences are those of
$G$, none tied. Perturb the objective of the occupancy polytope
$X(G_\tau)$ of \Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp} --- whose polytope half needs no
nondegeneracy, as \Cref{lem:tie-perturb} records --- lexicographically,
as in the proof of \Cref{lem:tie-perturb}: the LP orientation $t_\tau$ obtained is
Holt--Klee and agrees with $s$ at every $\sigma\in S_\tau$, where every
margin is strict and survives a small perturbation.
Faces: freezing Max coordinates leaves a stopping SSG whose outmap is the
face (\Cref{lem:subgame}), and faces of Holt--Klee orientations are
Holt--Klee. At $B^{2}$, non-Holt--Klee gives $\chi\ge2$ and the
realisation with $k=1$ gives $\chi\le2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Every class at $m=3$ is realised]\label{prop:m3-realised}
The $728$ acyclic unique sink orientations of the $3$-cube fall into $18$
classes under coordinate permutation and translation, $16$ of them
Holt--Klee. Each Holt--Klee class is the improvement orientation of a
nondegenerate one-player stopping SSG, on $28$ to $62$ vertices with
normal-form denominators $64$ and $128$ --- so at $m=3$ one-player realisability is
exactly the Holt--Klee condition, by \Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp} --- and each
of the two other classes is the improvement orientation of a nondegenerate
stopping SSG with three Max and six Min vertices, on $98$ and $170$
vertices --- and, by \Cref{prop:m3-one-min} below, with three Max and one
Min vertex. In particular the first blow-up level
$B(\text{$1$-cube})=(0,1,3,6,7,4,5,2)$ of \Cref{rem:blowup-measured} is
realised on $39$ vertices rather than $58$. (The route's readout systems
were found by linear programming and an evolution strategy and turned
into games by \Cref{thm:readout-realise}(b); the sixteen one-player games
and the $98$-vertex game were re-verified here from the game --- trap
test, exact value vectors as minima over all Min strategies, outmap, no
ties --- and both two-player games by the route's auditor; the eighteen
games are kept beside the repository's round-$16$ verification scripts.)
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}[Best-response pieces of the two realisations]\label{prop:pieces-measured}
All statements exact, recomputed here from the printed normal forms.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item $G^{\sharp}$ (\Cref{prop:auso-seven}; $m=4$, $k=2$). Index Min
      strategies by $\tau=\tau_{c_5}+2\tau_{c_6}$. The four frozen
      orientations are
      $t_{0}=(0,1,3,6,5,4,7,10,15,14,9,12,11,8,13,2)$,
      $t_{1}=(1,4,3,6,5,0,7,10,14,15,9,12,11,8,13,2)$,
      $t_{2}=(0,1,2,3,7,4,13,6,15,14,9,12,11,8,5,10)$,
      $t_{3}=(1,0,3,2,7,4,13,6,14,15,9,12,11,8,5,10)$,
      all four nondegenerate acyclic unique sink orientations, all four
      Holt--Klee, of heights $5,3,4,4$. The pieces are
      $S_{0}=\{0,1,7,13,15\}$, $S_{1}=\{2,3,8,9,12\}$,
      $S_{2}=\{5\}$, $S_{3}=\{4,6,10,11,14\}$. Along the
      height-$7$ run $8,6,11,7,13,5,1,0$ the best responses are
      $1,3,3,0,0,2,0$: five blocks, more than the $2^{k}=4$ available
      responses, so a response recurs.
\item $B^{2}$ (\Cref{prop:b2-realised}; $m=5$, $k=1$). The two frozen
      orientations are nondegenerate Holt--Klee acyclic unique sink
      orientations of heights $8$ and $4$; the pieces are
      $S_1=\{11,15,19,23,27,31\}$ and $S_0$ its complement; along the run
      $12,19,13,17,8,16,0,7,1,5,4$ the best response is $0$ except at
      $\sigma=19$, so the single Min vertex turns twice, and the run takes
      $9$ of its $10$ steps under $t_0$, more than $h(t_0)=8$: the one step
      under $t_1$ carries the walk from a point of $t_0$-height $3$ to one
      of $t_0$-height $8$, so no bound of the form ``steps under
      $\vartheta$ number at most $h(t_\vartheta)$'' holds.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}[The Holt--Klee defect, as far as it can be computed]\label{rem:hk-defect}
The invariant $\chi_{\mathrm{HK}}$ of \Cref{thm:min-count} asks for a
second Min vertex nowhere that can be computed. Every acyclic unique sink
orientation of the $4$-cube has $\chi_{\mathrm{HK}}\le2$: of the $12640$
classes of \Cref{prop:auso-census}, $6113$ are Holt--Klee and the other
$6527$ are pointwise selections from two Holt--Klee acyclic unique sink
orientations (exhaustive over the $4\,792\,176$ orientations and the
$2\,322\,704$ Holt--Klee ones, one class representative at a time; the
round-$16$ Min-budget route, reproduced by both its auditors). At $m=5$
the height-$12$ orientation $s_{12}$ of \Cref{prop:hstar-five} has
$\chi_{\mathrm{HK}}=2$ as well, witnessed by the Holt--Klee acyclic unique
sink orientations
\[
\begin{aligned}
 t_1=(&15,0,1,2,27,14,12,29,19,10,8,25,22,21,20,23,\\
      &31,18,16,17,11,30,28,5,7,26,24,13,3,6,4,9),\\
 t_2=(&7,18,0,1,19,30,4,5,15,26,8,13,27,22,12,25,\\
      &23,2,28,21,3,6,24,17,31,14,16,29,10,9,20,11),
\end{aligned}
\]
of heights $8$ and $4$, which agree with $s_{12}$ on the $21$-element set
$\{0,1,2,3,4,6,7,8,10,11,12,13,14,15,16,17,19,23,24,27,30\}$ and on the
$12$-element set $\{5,9,10,18,20,21,22,25,26,28,29,31\}$; the two overlap
at $10$ and cover the cube. So the greatest height of an orientation with
$\chi_{\mathrm{HK}}\le2$ is $h^{\ast}(m)$ for every $m\le5$, and
\Cref{thm:min-count} never forces $k\ge2$ there. Nor do the heights of
the pieces bound the height of a selection: $s_{G^{\sharp}}$, of height
$7$, is a pointwise selection from
$t_1=(0,1,2,3,15,4,13,6,8,9,10,11,7,12,5,14)$ and
$t_2=(1,4,3,6,7,0,5,10,14,15,9,12,11,8,13,2)$, Holt--Klee of heights $2$
and $3$, and the height-$6$ orientation
$(2,7,8,5,12,11,14,9,10,15,4,13,6,3,1,0)$ is a selection from two
Holt--Klee orientations of height $2$; over the $4$-cube the least sum of
piece heights over Holt--Klee covers of $s_{G^{\sharp}}$ is $5$, the excess
$h-\Sigma^{\ast}$ is at most $2$, and the distribution of $\Sigma^{\ast}$
over the $12640$ classes is $46,5367,7130,97$ at $\Sigma^{\ast}=2,3,4,5$.
Whether a dimension-free bound on the height of a selection in terms of
its pieces' heights exists is not settled by these two sizes.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[The Min-count bound is attained at $m=3$]\label{prop:m3-one-min}
Each of the two non-Holt--Klee classes of the $3$-cube is the improvement
orientation of a nondegenerate stopping SSG with three Max vertices and
\emph{one} Min vertex, so \Cref{thm:min-count}'s bound
$k\ge\log_2\chi_{\mathrm{HK}}=1$ is attained there and the six Min vertices
of \Cref{prop:m3-realised} are not needed. The harmonic normal forms, as
multiples of $\tfrac1{D}$ over the targets $(c_1,c_2,c_3,c_4,t_1)$ with
$c_4$ the Min vertex and the remaining mass going to $t_0$, are
\[
 \begin{array}{r@{\quad}l@{\qquad}l}
  \multicolumn{3}{l}{\text{(a) } s=(0,1,3,2,5,4,6,7),\ h=2,\ D=4096:}\\
  c_1: & (5,298,1722,0;\,2050)   & (0,0,1850,596;\,1643)\\
  c_2: & (0,188,3900,0;\,0)      & (176,176,2148,176;\,176)\\
  c_3: & (27,0,0,2682;\,1379)    & (0,582,0,2649;\,822)\\
  c_4: & (0,0,0,0;\,3959)        & (0,534,2800,75;\,679)\\[2pt]
  \multicolumn{3}{l}{\text{(b) } s=(0,1,3,2,6,7,5,4),\ h=3,\ D=256:}\\
  c_1: & (219,7,0,0;\,1)         & (201,8,0,46;\,0)\\
  c_2: & (0,0,153,0;\,86)        & (12,12,0,12;\,112)\\
  c_3: & (0,0,0,0;\,255)         & (0,0,0,194;\,0)\\
  c_4: & (0,0,0,0;\,15)          & (56,15,0,28;\,0).
 \end{array}
\]
Both were built into explicit fair-coin games by \Cref{lem:dyadic-row} ---
$145$ and $86$ vertices, three Max, one Min, $139$ resp.\ $80$ average and
the two sinks --- and verified from the game: greatest trap empty, the
eight $\val_\sigma$ taken as the componentwise minimum over both Min
strategies of exact profile values, no tied incidence, the outmap as
displayed, acyclic unique sink orientations of heights $2$ and $3$, not
Holt--Klee. In (a) the pieces are $S_0=\{0,1\}$, $S_1=\{2,\dots,7\}$ and
both frozen orientations are Holt--Klee of height $2$; in (b) they are
$S_0=\{0,1,2,3,6,7\}$, $S_1=\{4,5\}$ with frozen orientations
$(0,1,3,2,7,6,5,4)$ and $(1,0,3,2,6,7,5,4)$, Holt--Klee of heights $3$ and
$2$, and the longest run $4,2,1,0$ uses both pieces (verified here from
both games).
\end{proposition}

\subsection{The profile cube and its sink projection}\label{sec:projection}

\Cref{prop:gsharp-bigcube} looked at one instance of the two-player cube.
This subsection, from the round-$15$ sink-projection route, treats the
general case from scratch: the profile cube of every stopping game is a
unique sink orientation --- so \Cref{thm:cyclic-uso} no longer needs
Stickney and Watson for that --- its matrix is a $P$-matrix, the Max cube
is its sink projection, and Holt--Klee, the one structural constraint
known for it, constrains that projection not at all at the sizes we can
decide.

\begin{definition}[The profile orientation]\label{def:profile-orientation}
Let $G$ be stopping with controlled set $C=\Vmax\cup\Vmin$, $|C|=c$, in
harmonic normal form, so that action $a$ of $v\in C$ has the affine
readout $f_{v,a}(y):=q^{v,a}+\sum_wp^{v,a}_wy_w$ on $\mathbb R^{C}$, and
let $\varepsilon_v:=+1$ on $\Vmax$, $-1$ on $\Vmin$. For a profile
$\pi\in\{0,1\}^{C}$ let $y_\pi:=\val_\pi|_C$, the unique solution of
$y_v=f_{v,\pi(v)}(y)$ (\Cref{thm:contraction}), and put
\[
  s_C(\pi):=\{v\in C:\varepsilon_v(f_{v,\bar\pi(v)}(y_\pi)-y_\pi(v))>0\},\qquad
  D_C(\pi):=\{v\in C:f_{v,\bar\pi(v)}(y_\pi)=y_\pi(v)\},
\]
the out-set and the flat set at $\pi$: a coordinate is out exactly when
its owner strictly prefers its other action against the profile's own
value vector. $G$ is \emph{profile-nondegenerate} if every $D_C(\pi)$ is
empty; this is stronger than \Cref{def:improvement-uso}, which asks it of
the Max coordinates at Min's best responses only.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[One profile coordinate at a time]\label{lem:profile-trichotomy}
Let $\pi'=\pi\oplus e_v$. Exactly one of: (a) $v\in s_C(\pi)$, and then
$v\notin s_C(\pi')\cup D_C(\pi')$; (b) $v\in s_C(\pi')$, and then
$v\notin s_C(\pi)\cup D_C(\pi)$; (c) $v\in D_C(\pi)\cap D_C(\pi')$, and
then $y_{\pi'}=y_\pi$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $a=\pi(v)$, $b=\bar\pi(v)$, $x=y_\pi$, $y=y_{\pi'}$; the frozen
operators $T_\pi,T_{\pi'}$ differ only in row $v$. If
$f_{v,b}(x)=x(v)$ then $T_{\pi'}x=x$, so $y=x$ by uniqueness and
$f_{v,a}(y)=f_{v,b}(y)$: case (c). If $\varepsilon_v(f_{v,b}(x)-x(v))>0$,
say $v\in\Vmax$ (Min reverses every inequality), then $T_{\pi'}x\ge x$
strictly at $v$, so $x\le y$ by \Cref{cor:comparison} and
$y(v)=f_{v,b}(y)\ge f_{v,b}(x)>x(v)$; if $f_{v,a}(y)\ge y(v)$ then
$T_\pi y\ge y$ and $y\le x$, a contradiction, so $f_{v,a}(y)<y(v)$:
case (a). Case (b) is (a) with $\pi,\pi'$ exchanged.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Flat classes and face sinks on the profile cube]\label{lem:profile-faces}
Call the edge $\{\pi,\pi\oplus e_v\}$ flat when $v\in D_C(\pi)$ and let
the flat classes be the components of the flat graph. Then $s_C$ and
$D_C$ are constant on each class, the class of $\pi$ is the subcube
$\pi\oplus\operatorname{span}\{e_v:v\in D_C(\pi)\}$, and the classes are
the level sets of $\pi\mapsto y_\pi$. For a face $F$ with free set
$J\subseteq C$ let $G_J$ be the stopping subgame in which the coordinates
outside $J$ are frozen at their $F$-values; then
$\{\pi\in F:s_C(\pi)\cap J=\emptyset\}$ is nonempty, is the set of optimal
profiles of $G_J$, all carrying $y_\pi=\val(G_J)|_C$, and equals $A\cap F$
for a single flat class $A$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The first half is \Cref{lem:flat-class}(a),(b) with
\Cref{lem:profile-trichotomy} in place of \Cref{lem:trichotomy}; part (c)
of that lemma has no analogue, the profile cube being possibly cyclic.
For the second, freezing cannot create a trap, so $G_J$ is stopping
(\Cref{lem:trapchar}); at a profile of $F$ with no strictly switchable free
coordinate each player's chosen action attains the optimum of its two
options against $\val_\pi$, so $\val_\pi$ is a fixed point of the Shapley
operator of $G_J$ and equals $\val(G_J)$ by \Cref{thm:contraction}, making
$\pi$ an optimal pair; optimal pairs exist
(\Cref{thm:stopping-determinacy}) and all carry $\val(G_J)$, so they lie in
one flat class, and every member of $A\cap F$ has the same out-set.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The profile cube is a unique sink orientation]\label{thm:profile-uso}
Let $G$ be stopping. If $G$ is profile-nondegenerate, $s_C$ is the outmap
of a unique sink orientation of the $c$-cube. In general, choosing a
corner $c_A$ in each flat class $A$,
$s'_C(\pi):=s_C(\pi)\cup\{v\in D_C(\pi):\pi_v\ne(c_{A(\pi)})_v\}$ is the
outmap of a unique sink orientation with $s'_C(c_A)=s_C(c_A)$. Neither
need be acyclic (\Cref{thm:cyclic-uso}).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\Cref{thm:flat-resolution} with \Cref{lem:profile-trichotomy,lem:profile-faces}
substituted for \Cref{lem:trichotomy}, \Cref{lem:flat-class}(a),(b) and
\Cref{lem:face-sink}: its proofs of orientation and of unique sink use
nothing else, and its acyclicity paragraph, the only place
\Cref{lem:flat-class}(c) enters, is omitted.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Unique fixed points for arbitrary rewards]\label{lem:survival-contract}
Let $G$ be stopping, in harmonic normal form with rows $p^{v,a}$, and let
$S$ be the reduced survival operator
$(S\xi)_v=\max_a\sum_wp^{v,a}_w\xi_w$ on $\mathbb R^{C}$, the first-passage
analogue of \Cref{def:survival}; the argument applies verbatim to any
finite family of substochastic first-passage rows over $C$. Then
$S^{n}\bbone\to0$, so for arbitrary reals $\rho_{v,a}$ the operator
$T_\rho(y)_v:=\operatorname{opt}_a(\rho_{v,a}+\sum_wp^{v,a}_wy_w)$, $\max$
at Max and $\min$ at Min, has exactly one fixed point.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
$S^{n}\bbone$ decreases to a fixed point $\xi$ of $S$. If
$\mu:=\max\xi>0$, let $U:=\{\xi=\mu\}$; each $v\in U$ has an action with
$\sum_wp^{v,a}_w\xi_w=\mu$, forcing $\sum_wp^{v,a}_w=1$ and
$\operatorname{supp}p^{v,a}\subseteq U$, so the average part below
$v^{(a)}$ reaches no sink and lands in $U$; $U$ with those average
vertices is a nonempty trap, against \Cref{lem:trapchar}. So
$\|S^{n}\bbone\|_\infty\le\tfrac12$ for some $n$, and
$|T_\rho^{n}y-T_\rho^{n}y'|\le S^{n}|y-y'|$ makes $T_\rho^{n}$ a
contraction.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The complementarity form]\label{prop:lcp}
Let $G$ be stopping, fix a base profile $\beta$, and for $y\in\mathbb R^{C}$
put $z_v:=\varepsilon_v(y_v-f_{v,\beta(v)}(y))$ and
$w_v:=\varepsilon_v(y_v-f_{v,\bar\beta(v)}(y))$. Then $y\mapsto z$ is an
affine bijection, $w=Mz+q$ with
$M=E(I-Q_{\bar\beta})(I-Q_\beta)^{-1}E$ and
$q=E[(I-Q_{\bar\beta})(I-Q_\beta)^{-1}r_\beta-r_{\bar\beta}]$, where $Q$
and $r$ collect the rows $p$ and $q$ of the named actions and $E$ the
signs, and $y=\val|_C$ iff $z,w\ge0$ and $z^{\mathsf T}w=0$. Moreover
(a) $M$ is a $P$-matrix; (b) for every $S\subseteq C$ the complementary
basic solution with $z$ free on $S$ and $w$ free off $S$ exists, is
unique, and is the image of $y_\pi$ for $\pi=\beta\oplus S$; (c) at that
basis the free variable at $v$ is
$\varepsilon_v(f_{v,\pi(v)}(y_\pi)-f_{v,\bar\pi(v)}(y_\pi))$, negative
exactly when $v\in s_C(\pi)$ and zero exactly when $v\in D_C(\pi)$, so the
pivot orientation $S\mapsto\{v:\text{free variable}<0\}$ is $s_C$
translated by $\beta$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The complementary solutions are exactly the fixed points of the Shapley
operator on $C$, of which there is exactly one (\Cref{lem:survival-contract}). (a)
Letting $r_{\bar\beta}$ range over $\mathbb R^{C}$ lets $q$ range over all
of $\mathbb R^{C}$ while, by \Cref{lem:survival-contract}, the operator
keeps a unique fixed point; an LCP with a unique solution for every $q$
has a $P$-matrix, by the theorem of Samelson, Thrall and Wesler --- a
textbook fact imported here, from memory, and used nowhere else in this
document. (b) $y_\pi$ satisfies
$y_\pi(v)=f_{v,\pi(v)}(y_\pi)$, which reads $w_v=0$ on $S$ and $z_v=0$
off $S$, and the basic system $(I-Q_\pi)y=r_\pi$ has that unique
solution. (c) is the definition of $z,w$ read at $y_\pi$.
\end{proof}

This is the form in which \Cref{rem:lcp} cites Stickney and Watson, now
proved here; the identification (c) of the pivot orientation with the
switching orientation is presumably also in the literature on games and
$P$-matrix complementarity (G\"artner and R\"ust; Jurdzi\'nski and
Savani; from memory, unchecked), and we claim nothing for it.

\begin{proposition}[The Max cube is the sink projection]\label{prop:projection}
Let $G$ be stopping, $\sigma$ a Max strategy and
$F_\sigma:=\{\sigma\}\times\{0,1\}^{\Vmin}$. (a) The profiles
$\pi\in F_\sigma$ with $s_C(\pi)\cap\Vmin=\emptyset$ are exactly the pairs
$(\sigma,\tau)$ with $\tau$ a Min best response; there is one, and all
carry $y_\pi=\val_\sigma|_C$. (b) At every such $\pi$,
$s_C(\pi)\cap\Vmax=S_\sigma$ and $D_C(\pi)\cap\Vmax=D(\sigma)$, so the
improvement outmap of \Cref{def:improvement-uso} is
$\sigma\mapsto s_C(\pi)\cap\Vmax$, degenerate games included. (c) If $G$
is profile-nondegenerate the sink of $F_\sigma$ is unique and the
improvement orientation of the Max cube is the sink projection of the
profile cube along $\Vmin$; in general the sink projection of the
resolution $s'_C$ is the resolution of \Cref{thm:flat-resolution} with
the corners $\sigma\mapsto c_{A(\sigma)}|_{\Vmax}$. The projection is
acyclic whether or not the profile cube is (\Cref{prop:allsw-auso}, and
\Cref{thm:flat-resolution} in the degenerate case): on
the instance of \Cref{thm:cyclic-uso} the $6$-cycle meets the Min-face
sinks in three of its six profiles, whose arcs project to a directed
path, and it closes only through profiles at which Min has not
best-responded.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(a) is \Cref{lem:profile-faces} with $J=\Vmin$, the subgame being $G$
with Max frozen at $\sigma$. (b) $f_{i,a}(\val_\sigma|_C)=\val_\sigma(x_i^{(a)})$
because $f_{i,a}$ is the harmonic extension, so the comparison defining
$s_C$ at a Max coordinate is the one defining $S_\sigma$. (c) The flat
class of the sinks of $F_\sigma$ is determined by $\val_\sigma$, so
strategies in one Max flat class receive one corner, and the unique
$s'_C$-sink of $F_\sigma$ has Max out-set
$S_\sigma\cup\{i\in D(\sigma):\sigma_i\ne(c_A)_i\}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The height-seven orbit needs only one extra coordinate]\label{prop:seven-k1}
The orientation $s_{G^{\sharp}}$ of \Cref{prop:auso-seven}, which violates
Holt--Klee, is the sink projection along one coordinate of the Holt--Klee
acyclic unique sink orientation
\[
  (17,16,3,6,7,4,21,10,14,15,9,12,11,8,29,2,\;0,1,19,22,23,20,13,18,31,30,25,28,27,24,5,26)
\]
of the $5$-cube, of bottom-antipodal height $7$, whose two layers are
$s_{G^{\sharp}}$ with its values exchanged at $\{0,1\},\{6,14\}$ and at
$\{7,15\},\{8,9\}$ respectively and whose inner edges point up exactly
on $\{0,1,6,14\}$. (Szab\'o--Welzl on all pairs, acyclicity, Holt--Klee on
every face and the projection were re-verified here.)
\end{proposition}

So Holt--Klee is discharged by one extra coordinate wherever we can
check. At $m\le3$ every unique sink orientation of the $m$-cube is the
sink projection along one coordinate of a Holt--Klee one --- trivially
for the Holt--Klee ones, taking the orientation itself as one layer, the
comb pointing at its sink as the other and all inner edges towards the
first, and by explicit witnesses of the route for the two non-Holt--Klee
classes of the $3$-cube (its auditor re-verified all of them); at $m=4$
the extremal orbit is one by \Cref{prop:seven-k1}; and at $m=5$ the
second blow-up level $B^{2}$ is one by \Cref{prop:b2-realised}'s own
profile cube: the $6$-cube of its $64$ profiles is profile-nondegenerate,
a unique sink orientation, acyclic and Holt--Klee, of bottom-antipodal
height $9$ (verified here from the game, and by the route's auditor). Wherever
this can be checked --- $m\le3$, the height-$7$ orbit, $B^{2}$ ---
Holt--Klee therefore constrains nothing once
one Min vertex is present, and what a two-player realisation must satisfy
is a property not of the projection but of the matrix $M$ of
\Cref{prop:lcp}, $E(I-Q_1)(I-Q_0)^{-1}E$ with $Q_0,Q_1$ substochastic and
transient; ``which such matrices occur'' is where the two-player half of
\Cref{rem:auso-size} now sits. The handicap of \Cref{rem:lcp} is no
constraint either: the game of \Cref{thm:cyclic-uso} has $M+M^{\mathsf T}$
positive definite, at its all-zero base (principal minors
$\tfrac94,1,2,\tfrac{351}{256},\tfrac{30047}{16384},\tfrac{287}{256},\tfrac{31}{32}$)
and hence at every base (\Cref{rem:handicap-base}), and a cyclic profile
cube, while $G^{\sharp}$ and the one-player realisations of
\Cref{rem:blowup-measured} have positive handicap at every base profile
(the route's exact computations, re-verified by its auditor). Nor does
handicap zero bound the run:

\begin{proposition}[Handicap zero attains the one-player ceiling at $m\le4$]\label{prop:handicap-zero-ceiling}
At $m=3$ the $39$-vertex realisation of the first blow-up level
$(0,1,3,6,7,4,5,2)$ in \Cref{prop:m3-realised} has $M+M^{\mathsf T}$
positive definite at every base profile --- handicap zero --- while the
$58$-vertex realisation of the same orientation recorded after
\Cref{rem:blowup-measured} has positive handicap; both have all-switches
runs of length $4=h^{\ast}_{1}(3)$ (\Cref{cor:hstar-one}). At $m=4$ there
is a nondegenerate one-player stopping SSG $\mathrm{CVX}_6$ on $127$
vertices ($4$ Max, $121$ average, dyadic denominator $2^{7}$) with
handicap zero and an all-switches run of length $6=h^{\ast}_{1}(4)$; its
harmonic normal form over $(v_0,v_1,v_2,v_3;t_1)$, in units of
$\tfrac1{128}$ with the residue going to $t_0$, is
\[
  \begin{array}{lll}
   v_0:&(45,0,57,0;2),&(39,80,1,6;1)\\
   v_1:&(14,53,2,1;12),&(17,17,31,32;0)\\
   v_2:&(57,0,47,0;0),&(0,9,21,91;0)\\
   v_3:&(0,54,0,52;0),&(18,0,65,38;7),
  \end{array}
\]
its orientation is $(5,8,10,11,9,12,14,15,13,4,7,6,2,3,1,0)$ and its run
from $(0,1,0,0)$ switches
$\{v_1,v_3\},\{v_0,v_2,v_3\},\{v_2,v_3\},\{v_2\},\{v_0,v_1\},\{v_0\}$. So
one acyclic unique sink orientation has both a handicap-zero and a
positive-handicap realisation, handicap is not a property of the
improvement orientation, and at both values of $m$ where the one-player
ceiling is known exactly it is attained with $M+M^{\mathsf T}\succ0$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Everything was recomputed from the games in exact arithmetic: the
first-passage matrices $P_0,P_1$ over the Max vertices and the symmetric
matrix $B=\tfrac12(R_0^{\mathsf T}R_1+R_1^{\mathsf T}R_0)$, $R_a=I-P_a$,
which by \Cref{rem:handicap-base} is positive definite exactly when
$M+M^{\mathsf T}$ is, at every base. For the $39$-vertex game
$B=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-\frac{277}{4096}&-\frac{81}{128}\\
-\frac{277}{4096}&1&-\frac{89}{128}\\-\frac{81}{128}&-\frac{89}{128}&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$
with leading principal minors $1,\tfrac{16700487}{16777216},\tfrac{1744945}{33554432}$;
for the $58$-vertex game the third minor is
$-\tfrac{277907957}{68719476736}$ (the route's correctness auditor,
recomputed here). $\mathrm{CVX}_6$'s normal form is dyadic, so
\Cref{lem:dyadic-row} realises its rows by pruned binary trees of average
vertices, $127$ vertices in all; the game is stopping by
\Cref{lem:trapchar}, its leading principal minors are
$\tfrac{7625}{16384},\tfrac{47542061}{268435456},\tfrac{939760988311}{17592186044416},\tfrac{2290930799522897}{1152921504606846976}$,
all positive, none of its $64$ comparisons ties, and the run and outmap
are as stated (the round-$17$ variational route, its two auditors and
this round's root agent, \texttt{scripts/round17-verify/vr\_verify.py}
with the game files; a second $m=3$ example on $48$ vertices,
$\mathrm{CVX}_4$, is archived beside it, and three of the round-$15$
readout systems at $m=4$ also have handicap zero with run $6$, one built
on $181$ vertices by the auditor, so $\mathrm{CVX}_6$ is the smallest
known).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The handicap does not depend on the base profile]\label{rem:handicap-base}
In the harmonic normal form over $C$ let $P_a$ be the matrix of the
first-passage rows of the actions $a=0,1$, $R_a:=I-P_a$ (invertible, the
rows being substochastic and the game stopping,
\Cref{lem:survival-contract}), and $B:=\tfrac12(R_0^{\mathsf T}R_1+R_1^{\mathsf T}R_0)$.
The sum $q(x):=\sum_{v\in C}(x(v)-x(v^{(0)}))(x(v)-x(v^{(1)}))$ of the
complementarity forms printed after \Cref{thm:transport-objective} is the
gap $z^{\mathsf T}w$ of the complementarity problem of \Cref{prop:lcp}, its
Hessian on the affine space $\Psi(\mathbb R^{C})\supseteq Q(G)$ of
\Cref{lem:transport-dim} is $2B$ ($x(v)-x(v^{(a)})$ has linear part
$(R_ad)_v$ there), and $B$ is the congruence
$R_\beta^{\mathsf T}(EM_\beta E)R_\beta$ symmetrised, for every base
$\beta$ --- each $I-Q_\beta$ is invertible, its rows being first-passage
