rows (\Cref{lem:survival-contract}) --- because the $v$-th summand
$(R_0d)_v(R_1d)_v$ is symmetric in $v$'s two actions. Hence $M_\beta+M_\beta^{\mathsf T}\succeq0$ holds for
every base profile as soon as it holds for one, the handicap is zero at
every base or at none --- the invariance under principal pivot transforms
that \Cref{rem:lcp} records as prior art, here read off the normal form
--- and it is so exactly when
$\|(P_1-P_0)(I-\tfrac12(P_0+P_1))^{-1}\|_2\le2$, by
$d^{\mathsf T}Bd=\|(I-\bar P)d\|^{2}-\tfrac14\|(P_1-P_0)d\|^{2}$. On such
games $q$ is convex, $w^\ast$ is its unique minimiser over $Q(G)$ (it is
nonnegative there and vanishes only at $w^\ast$, by
\Cref{thm:transport-objective}), and the problem is a convex quadratic
programme --- the handicap-zero slice of the route \Cref{rem:lcp} surveys
and sets aside; the round-$17$ variational route found no member of it
outside the tractable classes of this document (every member it named has
$|C|\le2$ or one player), and none is claimed; membership is moreover a
property of the presentation, like $a$ in \Cref{prop:a-presentation}: the
subdivision below carries members out of it (the route's correctness
auditor, $16$ of $181$). Strict reversibility, the
hypothesis of the Dirichlet principle proper, is unavailable: if some
positive measure on the non-sinks makes every positional pair's chain
reversible, then the non-sink edge relation is symmetric, every component
of the graph it induces is a path (a cycle component would be a trap),
and a component carries at most one controlled vertex (two would trap
the segment between them by facing each other), so the game is a disjoint
union of birth--death chains with one choice each; while reversibility of
the chance part alone is vacuous, since inserting a Max vertex with two
equal successors into every edge out of an average vertex makes $\Vavg$
independent without changing stopping, values or the all-switches run
(\Cref{lem:splice}(a) run backwards).
\end{remark}

\subsection{Lemke's algorithm as a bias homotopy}\label{sec:bias}

The linear complementarity formulation of \Cref{prop:lcp} comes with an
algorithm, Lemke's, which \Cref{rem:lcp} left aside. The round-$16$
algorithmic free search read it in the game: it is a homotopy in a
loyalty bonus paid to a base profile: on one player an improving
multi-switch rule that none of the barriers of this document constrains,
on two players a directed path of the profile cube along which the value
vector need not be monotone.

\begin{definition}[The biased system]\label{def:bias}
Let $G$ be stopping in harmonic normal form on $C=\Vmax\cup\Vmin$
(\Cref{def:profile-orientation}), with readouts $f_{v,a}$ and signs
$\varepsilon_v$. Fix a \emph{base profile} $\beta\in\{0,1\}^{C}$ and a
positive \emph{covering vector} $d\in\mathbb R^{C}_{>0}$. For $\lambda\ge0$
put
\[
  f^{\lambda}_{v,a}(y)\;:=\;f_{v,a}(y)-\varepsilon_v d_v\lambda\,
     [\,a\ne\beta(v)\,],
\]
so that at every controlled vertex the base action is favoured by $d_v\lambda$
against its owner's alternative, and let
$T_\lambda(y)_v:=\operatorname{opt}_a f^{\lambda}_{v,a}(y)$ be the
corresponding operator on $\mathbb R^{C}$. Call a profile $\pi$
\emph{$\lambda$-optimal} if $y_\pi$, the unique solution of
$y_v=f^{\lambda}_{v,\pi(v)}(y)$, satisfies $T_\lambda y_\pi=y_\pi$.
By \Cref{lem:survival-contract} the operator $T_\lambda$ has exactly one
fixed point $y(\lambda)$ for every $\lambda$, the rewards being arbitrary
reals there, and $y(0)=\val|_C$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Lemke's algorithm is the bias homotopy]\label{thm:lemke-homotopy}
Let $G$ be stopping, $\beta$, $d$ as above, and let $M,q$ be the data of
\Cref{prop:lcp} for the base $\beta$; write $z,w$ for its variables. Then
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item for every $\lambda\ge0$ the pair $(z,w+\lambda d)$ solves
  $\mathrm{LCP}(M,q+\lambda d)$ if and only if the underlying $y$ is
  $y(\lambda)$; the solution is unique, $M$ being a $P$-matrix, so
  $\lambda\mapsto z(\lambda)$ is a well-defined piecewise affine path;
\item $\lambda_0:=\max_v\max\bigl(0,-q_v/d_v\bigr)$ is the least $\lambda$ at
  which $\beta$ is $\lambda$-optimal, and $\beta$ is $\lambda$-optimal for
  every $\lambda\ge\lambda_0$;
\item for $S\subseteq C$ the set $I_S$ of $\lambda\ge0$ whose solution has
  $z_v=0$ off $S$ and $(w+\lambda d)_v=0$ on $S$ is a closed interval, on
  which $z_S(\lambda)=-(M_{SS})^{-1}(q_S+\lambda d_S)$ is affine; the $I_S$
  cover $[0,\infty)$; when the homotopy is \emph{nondegenerate} --- no two
  distinct $S$ feasible on a common interval of positive length, which
  fails on an explicit $25$-vertex one-player game of the route's auditor
  --- they meet only in endpoints, $\lambda\mapsto S(\lambda)$ is a step
  function with at most $2^{|C|}-1$ breakpoints and pairwise distinct
  values, and the corresponding profiles $\beta\oplus S$ are pairwise
  distinct, consecutive ones differing in one coordinate when consecutive
  breakpoints are distinct; in general the path is the one Lemke's
  lexicographic tie-breaking selects;
\item Lemke's algorithm with covering vector $d$, started from the initial
  ray $z=0$, traverses exactly
  $\{(z(\lambda),w(\lambda)+\lambda d,\lambda):0\le\lambda\le\lambda_0\}$
  with $\lambda$ decreasing, and terminates at $\lambda=0$ with
  $y(0)=\val|_C$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(a) Write $w^{\lambda}:=w+\lambda d$. By \Cref{prop:lcp},
$z_v=\varepsilon_v(y_v-f_{v,\beta(v)}(y))=
\varepsilon_v(y_v-f^{\lambda}_{v,\beta(v)}(y))$ and
$w^{\lambda}_v=\varepsilon_v(y_v-f_{v,\bar\beta(v)}(y))+\lambda d_v
=\varepsilon_v(y_v-f^{\lambda}_{v,\bar\beta(v)}(y))$. Hence
$z,w^\lambda\ge0$ says that at a Max vertex $y_v$ dominates both biased
readouts and at a Min vertex is dominated by both, while
$z^{\mathsf T}w^{\lambda}=0$ says one of the two is attained at each vertex;
together these are $T_\lambda y=y$, whose solution is unique
(\Cref{lem:survival-contract}). Since $y\mapsto z$ is an affine bijection and
$w=Mz+q$, these conditions are $\mathrm{LCP}(M,q+\lambda d)$, and $M$ is a
$P$-matrix by \Cref{prop:lcp}(a).

(b) At $z=0$ the underlying $y$ is $y_\beta$ (\Cref{prop:lcp}(b) with
$S=\emptyset$) and $w^{\lambda}=q+\lambda d$, a solution iff
$q+\lambda d\ge0$.

(c) On $I_S$ the equations $w^{\lambda}_S=0$ read
$M_{SS}z_S+q_S+\lambda d_S=0$, determining $z_S$ affinely --- $M_{SS}$ is a
$P$-matrix, hence invertible --- and $I_S$ is cut out by the affine
inequalities $z_S(\lambda)\ge0$ and
$(Mz(\lambda)+q+\lambda d)_{C\setminus S}\ge0$, so it is an interval.
The $I_S$ cover $[0,\infty)$ by (a). Uniqueness of the solution does not
by itself make their interiors disjoint --- a complementary pair may
vanish together at a point and two supports then share an interval, as
on the $25$-vertex game named in the statement --- but the nondegeneracy
hypothesis says exactly that no two distinct $S$ are feasible on a common
interval of positive length, i.e.\ that the interiors are disjoint, and
then $\lambda\mapsto S(\lambda)$ is a step function with each $S$ on its
one interval $I_S$.

(d) Along an edge of Lemke's almost-complementary path the basis is
$\{z_v:v\in S\}\cup\{w_v:v\notin S\}\cup\{z_0\}$ and one complementary pair
is nonbasic, so every point of the edge is a \emph{complementary} solution of
$\mathrm{LCP}(M,q+\lambda d)$ for $\lambda=z_0$; by (a) the edge lies in the
graph of the function $\lambda\mapsto z(\lambda)$, so the path projects
injectively to $\lambda$. It starts on the initial ray, and its points
with $\lambda>\lambda_0$ are exactly that ray, by (b) and the injectivity;
so its other end is not a ray of unbounded $\lambda$, and not a ray of
bounded $\lambda$ either, since along such a ray $z$ would be unbounded
at one $\lambda$ against (a) and the affine form of (c). Hence the path
ends at a vertex, which for Lemke's pivoting means that $z_0=\lambda$
leaves the basis, at $\lambda=0$ (this replaces the classical ``no
secondary ray for a $P$-matrix'', which is not imported); being connected
it is the whole segment.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The homotopy is an exact algorithm]\label{cor:bias-algorithm}
On a stopping SSG the \emph{bias homotopy} --- start at $\pi_0=\beta$,
$\lambda=\lambda_0$; on the current piece solve
$y(\lambda)=\alpha+\lambda\gamma$ by two exact $|C|\times|C|$ linear systems;
take the largest $\lambda'\le\lambda$ at which some regret
$\rho_v(\lambda):=\varepsilon_v\bigl(y_v(\lambda)-
f^{\lambda}_{v,\bar\pi(v)}(y(\lambda))\bigr)$ vanishes and switch with
Lemke's lexicographic tie-breaking when several vanish at once (switching
a single one of them can stall at a degenerate breakpoint, on an
$8$-vertex instance of the route's auditor) --- terminates with $\val|_C$
after at most $2^{|C|}-1$ switches --- the
trivial enumeration ceiling, no better than trying every profile --- each
costing $O(|C|^{3})$ exact rational operations on numbers of polynomial
bit-size, and decides \Cref{prob:main}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:bias-attribution}
The reduction of turn-based stochastic games to $P$-matrix LCPs and the
Stickney--Watson correspondence are attributed in \Cref{rem:lcp,prop:lcp}
(G\"artner--R\"ust; Jurdzi\'nski--Savani; Hansen--Ibsen-Jensen). That the
Lemke path of a $P$-matrix LCP is the solution path of the one-parameter
family $\mathrm{LCP}(M,q+\lambda d)$ is standard in the complementarity
literature (Lemke; Cottle--Pang--Stone), reproved above only because the
exact form is needed; from memory, unchecked. The reading of $\lambda$ as
a \emph{loyalty bonus} paid to the base profile is, from memory and
unchecked against the sources, the Harsanyi--Selten linear tracing
procedure with prior $\beta$, whose complementary-pivoting path van den
Elzen and Talman identified with a Lemke path for bimatrix games and
which Herings and Peeters carried to stochastic games; what appears new
here is its statement on the harmonic normal form of an SSG,
\Cref{prop:bias-one-improving,lem:bias-path-lp} and the measurements.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[One player: an improving multi-switch rule]\label{prop:bias-one-improving}
Let $G$ be a stopping SSG with $\Vmin=\emptyset$, let
$\mu_1>\mu_2>\dots>\mu_K>\mu_{K+1}:=0$ be the breakpoints of the bias homotopy
and $\pi_0=\beta,\pi_1,\dots,\pi_K$ its profiles, so that $\pi_0$ is
$\lambda$-optimal on $[\mu_1,\infty)$ and $\pi_t$ on $[\mu_{t+1},\mu_t]$ for
$1\le t\le K$ (all these intervals nondegenerate: the coordinates that
flip at one value of $\lambda$ are counted as one breakpoint, so
consecutive profiles may differ in several coordinates).
Write $\kappa_\pi(v):=\sum_{w}d_w\,\mathbb E_\pi[N_w\mid v]\,[\pi(w)\ne\beta(w)]$
for the $d$-weighted expected number of visits to the deviating vertices.
Then for every $0\le t<K$,
\[
  \val_{\pi_{t+1}}-\val_{\pi_t}\;=\;\mu_{t+1}\bigl(\kappa_{\pi_{t+1}}-\kappa_{\pi_t}\bigr)\;\ge\;0
  \qquad\text{pointwise,}
\]
so the path is a nondecreasing chain of value vectors and every coordinate
in which $\pi_{t+1}$ differs from $\pi_t$ is strictly switchable at
$\pi_t$: each step is an improving switch of one or several vertices at
once, a single switch when the breakpoint is simple and a multi-switch
otherwise, so on one-player games the bias homotopy is a switching rule in
the sense of \Cref{def:rule} --- a multi-switch one, bounded by the
$N4^{a}$ of \Cref{thm:switch-count} rather than by its $N2^{a}$ --- and one
not among the seven of \Cref{prop:rules-fail}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
All $\varepsilon_v=+1$, so the biased value of a fixed profile is
$\val^{\lambda}_\pi=\val_\pi-\lambda\kappa_\pi$: the bias subtracts
$d_w\lambda$ at each visit to a deviating $w$. At $\lambda=\mu_{t+1}$ both
$\pi_t$ and $\pi_{t+1}$ are $\lambda$-optimal, hence both attain the optimal
value vector of that one-player game, giving
$\Delta v=\mu_{t+1}\Delta\kappa$ with
$\Delta v:=\val_{\pi_{t+1}}-\val_{\pi_t}$,
$\Delta\kappa:=\kappa_{\pi_{t+1}}-\kappa_{\pi_t}$. For
$\lambda\in[\mu_{t+2},\mu_{t+1})$, a nondegenerate interval, the profile
$\pi_{t+1}$ is optimal, so $\Delta v-\lambda\Delta\kappa\ge0$; substituting
the identity gives $(\mu_{t+1}-\lambda)\Delta\kappa\ge0$, hence
$\Delta\kappa\ge0$ and $\Delta v\ge0$. Moreover $\pi_t$ is not $\lambda$-optimal for $\lambda<\mu_{t+1}$, so
$\Delta v-\lambda\Delta\kappa$ is strictly positive somewhere there, whence
$\Delta\kappa\ne0$, $\Delta v\ne0$, and by \Cref{lem:profile-trichotomy}
every coordinate flipped at the breakpoint is strictly switchable: several
may flip at one breakpoint, so the path is a multi-switch improving rule
(the route's auditor: $77$ of $446$ steps on random one-player games flip
two or more coordinates, every flipped vertex strictly switchable).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[One player: the path is a shadow-vertex path]\label{prop:bias-shadow}
Let $G$ be a stopping SSG with $\Vmin=\emptyset$ and harmonic normal form
$(q^{v,a},p^{v,a})$ on $\Vmax$, $|\Vmax|=m$, and let
$\mathrm{Occ}(\mu)=\{f\ge0:\ \sum_{a}f_{v,a}-\sum_{u,a}f_{u,a}p^{u,a}_v=\mu_v\}$
be d'Epenoux's occupancy polytope for an initial distribution $\mu>0$ ---
by \Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp} a simple $m$-polytope with $2m$ facets whose
$2^{m}$ vertices are the deterministic profiles. Then $\pi$ is $\lambda$-optimal exactly when $f^{\pi}$
maximises $(c-\lambda e_\beta)\cdot f$ over $\mathrm{Occ}(\mu)$, where
$c_{v,a}:=q^{v,a}$ and $(e_\beta)_{v,a}:=d_v[a\ne\beta(v)]$. Hence the bias
homotopy is the parametric --- equivalently shadow-vertex --- simplex path of
that linear programme in the $2$-plane spanned by $c$ and $e_\beta$, started
at the vertex $f^{\beta}$, and its length is the number of vertices of the
shadow of $\mathrm{Occ}(\mu)$ in that plane between $f^{\beta}$ and the
optimum.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The occupancy correspondence and the identification of vertices with
deterministic profiles is d'Epenoux's, already used in
\Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp}; $\lambda$-optimality of $\pi$ is optimality of the
policy in the one-player game with rewards $c-\lambda e_\beta$, which for
$\mu>0$ is optimality of $f^{\pi}$ in the linear programme.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Two players: a directed path of the profile cube, not a value-monotone one]\label{prop:bias-two-nonmono}
The identity $\val_{\pi_{t+1}}-\val_{\pi_t}=\mu_{t+1}
(\kappa^{\varepsilon}_{\pi_{t+1}}-\kappa^{\varepsilon}_{\pi_t})$, with
$\kappa^{\varepsilon}_\pi(v)=\sum_w\varepsilon_wd_w\mathbb E_\pi[N_w\mid v]
[\pi(w)\ne\beta(w)]$, holds on every stopping SSG by the same proof, but
its right-hand side has no sign when both players are present, and the
value vector is measurably not monotone along the path (exact: at $99+66$
of $181+137$ two-player steps on random $10$- and $13$-vertex stopping
games some coordinate of $\val_\pi$ fell, while every one-player step
raised none). What survives with two players is the profile cube: every
step of the homotopy flips a coordinate that lies in the out-map
$s_C(\pi_t)$ of the profile orientation of \Cref{thm:profile-uso}, so
the path is a directed path of that unique sink orientation (the route's
auditor, exact: every step of every path from all $64$ base profiles of
$G^{\sharp}$ and of the game of \Cref{prop:b2-realised}, and all $3612$
steps on $400$ random stopping games). It is therefore not a switching
rule of \Cref{def:rule} on the Max cube, and neither
\Cref{cor:no-height,thm:impedance,thm:normalform-barrier,thm:window-barrier}
nor \Cref{thm:matching-barrier,thm:slack-barrier,thm:seeded-barrier,thm:ratio-sandwich}
speaks about it; it is also not residue-blind, reading as it does the whole
harmonic normal form.
\end{proposition}

\begin{lemma}[Realising a prescribed path is one linear programme]\label{lem:bias-path-lp}
Fix the transition part $(Q_\beta,Q_{\bar\beta})$ of a harmonic normal form on
$C$ of a \emph{stopping} game (so that every mixed profile is transient),
the signs $\varepsilon$ and the covering vector $d$; then
$M=E(I-Q_{\bar\beta})(I-Q_\beta)^{-1}E$ is fixed and
$q=MEr_\beta-Er_{\bar\beta}$ is affine in the reward vectors. Let
$S_0=\emptyset,S_1,\dots,S_K$ be subsets of $C$ with
$|S_t\triangle S_{t-1}|=1$. Then the reward vectors and breakpoints
$\lambda_1>\dots>\lambda_K>0$ for which the bias homotopy has exactly that
sequence of supports are the solutions of the linear system
\[
  0\le r_{\beta}\le\bbone-Q_\beta\bbone,\quad
  0\le r_{\bar\beta}\le\bbone-Q_{\bar\beta}\bbone,\quad
  q+\lambda_1d\ge0,
\]
\[
  z_{S_t}(\lambda)\ge0\ \text{and}\ \bigl(Mz(\lambda)+q+\lambda d\bigr)_i\ge0\ (i\notin S_t)
  \quad\text{at }\lambda=\lambda_t\text{ and }\lambda=\lambda_{t+1},\ 1\le t\le K,
\]
with $\lambda_{K+1}:=0$ and $z_{S}(\lambda)=-(M_{SS})^{-1}(q_S+\lambda d_S)$,
all of whose left-hand sides are affine in
$(r_\beta,r_{\bar\beta},\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_K)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
$z_S$ and $w$ are affine in $(q,\lambda)$ by
\Cref{thm:lemke-homotopy}(c), and $q$ is affine in the rewards by
\Cref{prop:lcp}; an affine function of $\lambda$ nonnegative at both
endpoints of an interval is nonnegative on it; and by uniqueness of the
solution, ``$z_{S}(\lambda)\ge0$ and $w_i(\lambda)\ge0$ off $S$'' says exactly
that the solution at $\lambda$ is the complementary basic solution with basis
$S$, i.e.\ that the profile there is $\beta\oplus S$. The reward bounds are
the constraints $q^{v,a}\ge0$, $q^{v,a}+\sum_wp^{v,a}_w\le1$ of a harmonic
normal form.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The named families do not defeat the homotopy]\label{rem:bias-families}
With $d=\bbone$ and the maximum over all $2^{|C|}$ base profiles, the
number of breakpoints is: $L_n$ ($n\le10$) exactly $n$; $\mathrm{RC}(k)$
($k\le10$) exactly $k$; $M_n$ ($n=3,4,5$) exactly $2n+1=|C|$; the
$19$-vertex instance $G^{\ast}$ of the harness ($|C|=7$) $6$ distinct
breakpoints, $9$ coordinate flips;
$\mathrm{WD}$, $\mathrm{CC}$, $H_m$, $G_m$ ($|C|\le2$) at most $3$;
$G^{\sharp}$ and the game of \Cref{prop:b2-realised} ($|C|=6$) $12$ each,
attained at one base profile (the route's auditor); and $P_D$ of
\Cref{thm:fold} $0,2,4,8,10,13$ coordinate flips at $D=1,\dots,6$ (the
route's auditor; the route reported $0,2,4,6,10,12$, and counting
distinct breakpoints gives $0,1,2,3$ at $D\le4$), so at $D=6$ the count
$13$ exceeds $|C|=12$ by one --- the convention matters because $P_D$ is
profile-degenerate, its $F_d$ and $G_d$ sharing a successor pair. So no
hard family of this document costs the homotopy more than about $2|C|$
switches; in particular the ladder, on which least-index and
smallest-gap need $2^{n}-1$, costs it $n$, and $\mathrm{BP}(D)$ of
\Cref{prop:discount-fold}, whose stopping-probability path has $2^{D}-1$
breakpoints, costs it $1,2,3,4,5$ at $D\le5$ under the single-switch
tie-breaking and $1,2,3,6,7$ when every vanishing regret is switched
(measured, $|C|=2D$; the round-$17$ parametric-path route).
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[The path exceeds $|C|$; four exact witnesses]\label{prop:bias-witnesses}
Let $\mathrm{LP}^{\ast}(m)$ be the greatest number of homotopy breakpoints,
with $d=\bbone$, over stopping SSGs with $|C|=m$ and over all base profiles.
Then, witnessed by explicit \emph{one-player} stopping SSGs verified from the
game,
\[
  \mathrm{LP}^{\ast}(1)=1,\quad \mathrm{LP}^{\ast}(2)=3=2^{2}-1,\quad
  \mathrm{LP}^{\ast}(3)\ge7=2^{3}-1,\quad
  \mathrm{LP}^{\ast}(4)\ge12,\quad \mathrm{LP}^{\ast}(5)\ge18,\quad
  \mathrm{LP}^{\ast}(6)\ge22 .
\]
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item $\mathrm{HAM}_3$, on $35$ vertices ($3$ Max, $30$ average, normal-form
  denominator $2^{4}$), base profile $\beta=(1,0,0)$: the homotopy visits
  \emph{all eight} profiles,
  $100\to110\to010\to000\to001\to011\to111\to101$, a Hamiltonian path in the
  profile cube, and Lemke makes $8$ pivots. Its normal form is
  \[
  \begin{array}{l|ll}
   v & f_{v,0} & f_{v,1}\\\hline
   1 & \tfrac18+\tfrac58y_1+\tfrac18y_2 & \tfrac1{16}y_1+\tfrac{11}{16}y_3\\
   2 & \tfrac58y_1+\tfrac5{16}y_2 & \tfrac18+\tfrac1{16}y_1+\tfrac7{16}y_3\\
   3 & \tfrac14+\tfrac7{16}y_3 & \tfrac1{16}+\tfrac{15}{16}y_3
  \end{array}
  \]
  with $\val|_C=(\tfrac{11}{15},\tfrac23,1)$ and breakpoints
  $\tfrac{47}{1080},\tfrac{391}{9720},\tfrac{283}{7416},\tfrac5{144},
   \tfrac{29}{876},\tfrac{71}{2216},\tfrac7{232}$;
\item $\mathrm{PATH}_4$, on $233$ vertices ($4$ Max, $227$ average),
  $\beta=(0,0,0,0)$: the homotopy visits $13$ of the $16$ profiles,
  $0000\to0100\to1100\to1000\to1001\to1011\to1010\to1110\to1111\to0111\to
  0110\to0010\to0011$; Lemke makes $13$ pivots;
\item $\mathrm{PATH}_5$, on $232$ vertices ($5$ Max, $225$ average),
  $\beta=(0,0,0,0,1)$: the homotopy visits $19$ of the $32$ profiles; Lemke
  makes $19$ pivots;
\item $\mathrm{PATH}_6$, on $300$ vertices ($6$ Max, $292$ average),
  $\beta=(0,0,0,0,1,0)$: the homotopy visits $23$ of the $64$ profiles; Lemke
  makes $23$ pivots.
\end{enumerate}
$\mathrm{PATH}_6$ is $\mathrm{PATH}_5$ with a sixth Max vertex adjoined,
so the four are three independent data points and not a family with a
growth law. All four were built from their dyadic normal forms by \Cref{lem:dyadic-row}
(a row absorbed entirely in $t_1$, like $\mathrm{HAM}_3$'s $(v_3,1)$,
does not leak, and stopping is then the trap test directly)
and verified \emph{from the game}: stopping by \Cref{lem:trapchar}, the normal
form recomputed from the successor lists, the value against brute force over
all profiles, and the path by two independent implementations --- the
game-side homotopy and a lexicographic Lemke tableau on $(M,q)$, whose pivot
count is one more than the number of breakpoints. In particular no bound
$\mathrm{LP}\le|C|$ and no bound $\mathrm{LP}\le2|C|$ holds.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}[One Max vertex, any dyadic breakpoint set of the stopping-probability path]\label{prop:one-vertex-path}
Let $R=\{r_1<\dots<r_k\}\subset(0,1)$ be dyadic rationals,
$P(\varrho):=\prod_i(\varrho-r_i)=2^{-K_0}\sum_dA_d\varrho^{d}$ with
$A_d\in\mathbb Z$, and $K$ such that $2^{K}\ge\sum_d|A_d|$. There is a
one-player stopping SSG $\mathrm{OS}(R)$ with a single Max vertex $u$ whose
two actions lead to gadgets realising the laws $(A_d)_{+}2^{-K}$ and
$(A_d)_{-}2^{-K}$ on a shared deterministic chain into $t_1$, shifted by
$K$, so that in $\mathrm{OS}(R)_{\varrho}$ (\Cref{def:discount-path})
\[
  \val(u^{(0)})-\val(u^{(1)})=2^{K_0-K}\varrho^{K}P(\varrho).
\]
Hence the stopping-probability path has breakpoint set exactly $R$, each
breakpoint a reversal of $u$, and for $k\ge2$ it returns to a pair it has
left. With $R=\{i2^{-t}:1\le i\le k\}$, $t=\lceil\log_2(k+1)\rceil$, one has
$K=O(k\log k)$ and $|V|=O(k^{2}\log k)$, so a single controlled vertex
reverses $\Omega(\sqrt{N/\log N})$ times and $B(G)$ is bounded by no
function of $|C|$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By \Cref{lem:dyadic-row} a dyadic sub-probability row of denominator
$2^{K}$ over $\ell$ targets is a pruned binary tree of at most $\ell K$
average vertices whose surviving edge to the $d$-th target is entered after
exactly $j\le K$ steps; redirecting that edge into position $d+K-j$ of one
shared chain $q_L\to\dots\to q_1\to t_1$ ($q_i$ average with both successors
$q_{i-1}$, $\val(q_i)=\varrho^{i}$) makes the token reach $t_1$ after exactly
$d+K$ steps. Every vertex is average and every path reaches a sink, so the
game is acyclic and stopping (\Cref{lem:trapchar}), and the two readouts are
$2^{-K}\sum_d(A_d)_{\pm}\varrho^{K+d}$, whose difference is the display. Its
zeros in $(0,1)$ are $r_1,\dots,r_k$, all simple, and with $|C|=1$ the sign
of the difference decides $u$ (\Cref{rem:discount-path}), so $u$ reverses
exactly $k$ times. As for the size, $\sum_d|A_d|=2^{K_0}\prod_i(1+r_i)\le2^{K_0+k}$,
so $K\le K_0+k\le tk+k$; the two trees cost at most $2(k+1)K$ vertices and
the chain $K+k$. (Built and verified from the game by the round-$17$
parametric-path route for $k\le8$, $|V|=8,17,32,61,94,129,169,250$; the
instances with $R=\{\tfrac14,\tfrac12\}$, $17$ vertices, difference
$\tfrac12\varrho^{4}(\varrho-\tfrac14)(\varrho-\tfrac12)$, and
$R=\{\tfrac18,\dots,\tfrac58\}$, $94$ vertices, difference
$\tfrac18\varrho^{15}\prod(\varrho-r_i)$, were recomputed here from their
successor lists, \texttt{scripts/round17-verify/bp\_verify.py}.)
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The two homotopies differ]\label{cor:two-homotopies}
The stopping-probability path is not the bias homotopy of \Cref{def:bias}
in another parametrisation: on $\mathrm{OS}(\{\tfrac14,\tfrac12\})$ the path
is $0,1,0$, whereas with $|C|=1$ the bias homotopy has at most $2^{1}-1=1$
breakpoint and pairwise distinct profiles (\Cref{thm:lemke-homotopy}(c)).
More generally the bias path cannot return to a support it has left, each
$I_S$ being an interval (\Cref{thm:lemke-homotopy}(c), with no
nondegeneracy hypothesis), and neither can the shadow-vertex path of
\Cref{prop:bias-shadow}, since for a parametric linear programme
$\max\,(c+\lambda e)\cdot f$ over a fixed polytope the set of $\lambda$ at
which a given vertex is optimal is an interval, its defining inequalities
being affine in $\lambda$; a parameter in the transition matrix can return,
and the fold of \Cref{prop:discount-fold} needs exactly that. On
$\mathrm{BP}(D)$ the bias homotopy was measured at $1,2,3,4,5$ breakpoints
for $D\le5$ against the discount path's $2^{D}-1$ (\Cref{rem:bias-families});
whether that separation is exponential is not proved, the only proved
ceiling for the bias path there being $2^{|C|}-1=4^{D}-1$.
\Cref{prop:leapfrog} is the
neighbouring construction on the all-switches side, one Max vertex
switched $K$ times on $O(K^{2})$ vertices.
\end{corollary}

\begin{remark}[Exactly what is missing]\label{rem:bias-gap}
One statement: \emph{there are $c>0$ and stopping SSGs $G_n$ with
$|C(G_n)|=n$, a base profile and covering vector $\bbone$, on which the bias
homotopy makes at least $2^{cn}$ switches.} By \Cref{prop:bias-shadow} its
one-player half asks for a two-action Markov decision process whose occupancy
polytope has an exponentially long shadow in a direction selecting one
action-coordinate per state; by \Cref{prop:bias-one-improving} such a family would
also be an exponential lower bound for an improving multi-switch rule that is
none of the seven of \Cref{prop:rules-fail} and is not all-switches.
Conversely a polynomial bound on the path would, by
\Cref{thm:lemke-homotopy}, put \textsc{SSG-Value} in $\mathsf P$, so no
polynomial bound may be conjectured here.

The obstruction met in the search is concrete, and can be stated as a lemma.
Let an outer Max vertex $u$ be added to a block $B$ so that both of $u$'s
actions enter $B$ at the same distribution up to a positive scalar: action $x$
sends the token into $B$ with law $c_x\mu$ and pays $r_{u,x}$. Then for a
policy $(x,\tau)$ the shadow coordinates are
$\kappa=[x{=}1]+c_x\kappa_{\mathrm{in}}(\mu,\tau)$ and
$\val=r_{u,x}+c_x\val_{\mathrm{in}}(\mu,\tau)$, so each of the two copies of
the inner point set is a \emph{homothety} of it: the two copies carry
identical slope sequences $s_1>\dots>s_{k-1}$, where $k$ is the number of
vertices of the inner hull. If the copies are separated in $\kappa$ --- which
they are as soon as the $+1$ from $u$'s own occupancy dominates --- the joint
hull takes a prefix of length $p$ of one and a suffix of length $q$ of the
other, and concavity forces $s_{p-1}>s_{k-q+1}$, i.e.\ $p+q\le k+1$: the
doubling gains \emph{at most one} vertex. Doubling therefore requires the two
actions of $u$ to enter $B$ with \emph{non-proportional} weightings, so that
the second copy's slopes are compressed below the first's. That is what
$\mathrm{HAM}_3$ achieves at $m=3$; iterating it is open.
The measured growth of $\mathrm{LP}^{\ast}$ is
$1,3,7,\ge12,\ge18,\ge22$ at $m=1,\dots,6$, against the ceiling
$2^{m}-1=1,3,7,15,31,63$: the ceiling is attained at $m\le3$ and the
successive differences are $2,4,5,6,\ge4$, the last search being the least
converged. Nothing may be read into the shape of that sequence:
every search in this project that looked for a hard instance failed until the
instance was engineered, and a quadratic bound on $\mathrm{LP}^{\ast}$ would
put \textsc{SSG-Value} in $\mathsf P$, so it must not be conjectured. What the
numbers do establish is the lower bounds above; they were obtained by local
search on the \emph{transition structure} with the rewards eliminated by
\Cref{lem:bias-path-lp}, and by local search on the reduced game scored by the
exact path length, in both cases followed by dyadic rounding and
re-verification from the built game --- not by sampling raw games, which never
exceeded $|C|+2$.
\end{remark}

\subsection{A rule that needs exponentially many switches}

\Cref{thm:short-path} says a path of at most $|\Vmax|$ single improving
switches to $\Opt$ always exists. It says nothing about a rule finding
one, and the most classical rule of all misses it by an exponential
margin, on an acyclic stopping game with no Min vertices whatever.

\begin{definition}[The ladder $L_n$]\label{def:ladder}
For $n\ge1$ let $L_n$ be the SSG with $\Vmax=\{v_1,\dots,v_n\}$,
$\Vavg=\{w_1,\dots,w_n\}$, $\Vmin=\emptyset$ and, with the conventions
$v_{n+1}:=t_0$ and $w_{n+1}:=t_1$,
\[
  v_i\longrightarrow\{v_{i+1},\,w_{i+1}\},\qquad
  w_i\longrightarrow\{v_{i+1},\,w_{i+1}\}\qquad(1\le i\le n),
\]
the first-listed successor being $v_{i+1}$. Then $|V(L_n)|=2n+2$, every
edge runs from level $i$ to level $i+1$, so $L_n$ is acyclic and a sink
is reached within $n+1$ steps under every pair: $L_n$ is stopping.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[The least-index rule needs $2^{n}-1$ switches]\label{thm:ladder}
Identify a Max strategy of $L_n$ with $b\in\{0,1\}^n$, where $b_i=0$ means
$v_i\mapsto v_{i+1}$. Started from $b=0\cdots0$, single-switch strategy
improvement under the \emph{least-index} rule --- switch the switchable
vertex of smallest index --- performs exactly $2^{n}-1$ iterations and
visits all $2^{n}$ strategies, each once. The \emph{smallest-improvement-gap}
rule, which refers to no vertex numbering, performs the same run. Since
$n=(N-2)/2$, both take $2^{(N-2)/2}-1$ iterations on a stopping SSG with
$N$ vertices.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
There are no Min vertices and the graph is acyclic, so $\val_b$ is
computed by back-substitution: writing $V_i,W_i$ for its values at
$v_i,w_i$ and $V_{n+1}=0$, $W_{n+1}=1$,
\[
  W_i=\tfrac12\bigl(V_{i+1}+W_{i+1}\bigr),\qquad
  V_i=\begin{cases}V_{i+1},&b_i=0,\\ W_{i+1},&b_i=1.\end{cases}
\]
Put $d_i:=W_i-V_i$, so $d_{n+1}=1$. If $b_i=0$ then
$d_i=\tfrac12(V_{i+1}+W_{i+1})-V_{i+1}=\tfrac12 d_{i+1}$, and if $b_i=1$
then $d_i=-\tfrac12 d_{i+1}$. Hence, with
$\pi_i:=\sum_{j\ge i}b_j \bmod 2$,
\[
  d_i=(-1)^{\pi_i}\,2^{\,i-n-1}.
\]
Vertex $v_i$ is strictly switchable exactly when its other successor has
strictly larger value: for $b_i=0$ that reads $W_{i+1}>V_{i+1}$, i.e.
$d_{i+1}>0$, i.e. $\pi_{i+1}=0$; for $b_i=1$ it reads $d_{i+1}<0$, i.e.
$\pi_{i+1}=1$. In both cases $\pi_i=b_i+\pi_{i+1}\equiv0$, so
\[
  S_b=\{\,i:\pi_i=0\,\}.
\]
The map $b\mapsto\pi$ is triangular over $\mathbb{F}_2$ with unit
diagonal, hence a bijection of $\{0,1\}^n$, so
$R(b):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_i2^{\,i-1}$ is a bijection onto
$\{0,\dots,2^n-1\}$. Flipping $b_j$ complements $\pi_i$ for exactly the
indices $i\le j$, i.e. replaces $R$ by $R\oplus(2^{j}-1)$.

Let $j:=\min S_b$. Then $\pi_1=\dots=\pi_{j-1}=1$ and $\pi_j=0$, that is,
bits $0,\dots,j-2$ of $R$ are $1$ and bit $j-1$ is $0$; so
$R\oplus(2^{j}-1)$ clears bits $0,\dots,j-2$ and sets bit $j-1$, which is
precisely $R+1$. Each least-index switch therefore increments $R$ by one.
At $b=0\cdots0$ every $\pi_i=0$, so $R=0$; and the run halts exactly when
$S_b=\emptyset$, i.e. when every $\pi_i=1$, i.e. at $R=2^n-1$. Hence
exactly $2^n-1$ switches, through $2^n$ pairwise distinct strategies.

For the gap rule, the gain at a switchable $v_i$ is
$|W_{i+1}-V_{i+1}|=|d_{i+1}|=2^{\,i-n}$, strictly increasing in $i$. The
smallest gap is thus attained at the least index, and uniquely, so the
two rules agree step for step. Both counts were confirmed in exact
rational arithmetic for $n=1,\dots,12$, giving
$1,3,7,15,31,63,127,255,511,1023,2047,4095$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:ladder}
Three things are worth separating. First, $L_n$ is as easy as an SSG can
be --- acyclic, one-player, solvable by back-substitution in linear time
--- so this is a lower bound against a \emph{rule}, not evidence about the
problem. Second, and this is an attribution the theorem owes: the pair
``exponential for the least-index rule, linear for all-switches, on a
two-action instance'' is Melekopoglou and Condon's, whose $2^{n}-1$ lower
bound for the simple (least-index) policy-improvement rule on Markov
decision processes with two actions per state is the published source of
this phenomenon, and whose instances are one-player games of exactly this
shape. \Cref{thm:ladder} should be read as that result transcribed into
\Cref{def:ssg}'s model, with the smallest-gap rule added; the open
question they leave --- whether Howard's rule is superpolynomial with two
actions per state --- is precisely the one-player half of the pivot of
\Cref{sec:refutations}. The all-switches rule terminates on $L_n$ in exactly $n$
iterations (exact computation, $n=1,\dots,12$), so least-index and
all-switches are exponentially separated here, in the direction opposite
to \Cref{thm:all-switches-refuted} and \Cref{prop:allswitch-overshoot};
no rule in this family dominates another. Third, along the run the value
\emph{vector} increases strictly at every step, as it must by
\Cref{lem:switch}, while the single value $\val_b(v_1)$ increases only weakly: it is
nondecreasing along the run but stalls at every second step ---
$0,\tfrac18,\tfrac18,\tfrac14,\tfrac14,\dots,\tfrac78,\tfrac78,1$ at
$n=4$, exact arithmetic for $n\le5$. The contrast with \Cref{thm:short-path} is in fact
as extreme as it can be. Here $\Opt=\{0\cdots01\}$ and
$w^\ast(v_1)=1$: optimal play walks the $v$-chain to $v_n$ and steps to
$t_1$. Every $v_i$ is switchable at $b=0\cdots0$, and switching the
\emph{last} one reaches $\Opt$ in a single step. So from the same
strategy the shortest improving route has length $1$ and the least-index
route has length $2^{n}-1$; a breadth-first search over the improving-switch
digraph confirms the distance is $1$ for $n=1,\dots,12$. This is the
sharpest form of \Cref{cor:selection}: not merely that a short route
exists while a natural rule misses it, but that the rule can miss a
one-step route by an exponential margin, and that no scalar read off the
start vertex distinguishes the two.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[A run stops informing after $|C|+1$ rounds]\label{rem:run-informs}
The rank argument of \Cref{thm:eval-queries} applies to \emph{any}
sequence of strategies, a run of any rule: with
$R_s:=\operatorname{span}\{(1,\val_{\sigma^{r}}|_C):r\le s\}$, the vector
$(1,\val_{\sigma^{s}}|_C)$ lies outside $R_{s-1}$ for at most $d(G)+1\le|C|+1$
indices $s$, and at every other index the value vector and all its
appeals are already determined, every harmonic system consistent with the
earlier answers predicting them exactly. On the least-index run of $L_6$,
$64$ strategies long, exactly $7$ rounds carry information and the other
$57$ are predicted, values and appeals, by any system consistent with the
first seven ($L_3,\dots,L_6$: $4,5,6,7$ informative rounds out of
$8,16,32,64$; the round-$17$ query-model route, exact arithmetic). An
exponentially long run spends all but $|C|+1$ of its rounds recomputing
consequences of what its first few evaluations implied; the rule cannot
see that, because it reads only the orientation.
\end{remark}

The ladder is in fact extremal, and the reason is the sharp denominator
bound: each $\val_\sigma$ lies on a grid of mesh $1/D_\sigma$ with
$D_\sigma\le2^{a}$, so a single improving switch, which compares two
coordinates of one value vector, cannot gain less than $2^{-a}$, a
multi-switch step, which compares coordinates of two vectors, less than
$4^{-a}$, and no improving rule whatever can run longer than that allows.

\begin{theorem}[Every single-switch improving rule stops within $N2^{a}$ switches, every multi-switch one within $N4^{a}$]\label{thm:switch-count}
Let $G$ be a stopping SSG with $a=|\Vavg|$, and let
$\sigma_0,\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_L$ be any sequence of Max strategies with
$\sigma_{j+1}=\sigma_j[v_j]$ for some strictly switchable $v_j\in
S_{\sigma_j}$. Then $L\le N2^{a}$. If instead each step switches an
arbitrary nonempty subset of $S_{\sigma_j}$ --- the all-switches rule
included --- then $L\le N4^{a}$. Both bounds are independent of
$|\Vmax|$ and $|\Vmin|$, and the first is tight to within a factor $N$,
by \Cref{thm:ladder}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $x_j:=\val_{\sigma_j}$ and $\Phi(x):=\sum_{u\in V}x(u)\in[0,N]$. By
\Cref{lem:switch}, $x_{j+1}\ge x_j$ pointwise, so
$\Phi(x_{j+1})-\Phi(x_j)\ge x_{j+1}(v_j)-x_j(v_j)$. Since $x_{j+1}$ is a
fixed point of $T_{\sigma_{j+1}}$ and $\sigma_{j+1}(v_j)$ is the
successor $\bar\sigma_j(v_j)$ not used by $\sigma_j$,
\[
  x_{j+1}(v_j)=x_{j+1}\bigl(\bar\sigma_j(v_j)\bigr)\ \ge\ x_j\bigl(\bar\sigma_j(v_j)\bigr),
\]
so $\Phi(x_{j+1})-\Phi(x_j)\ge x_j(\bar\sigma_j(v_j))-x_j(\sigma_j(v_j))$,
which is positive because $v_j$ is strictly switchable. By
\Cref{thm:stopping-determinacy} there is a positional $\tau_j$ with
$x_j=v_{\sigma_j,\tau_j}$, so by \Cref{lem:denominator-sharp} all
coordinates of $x_j$ are rationals with a common denominator
$D_j\le2^{a}$; a positive difference of two of them is therefore at least
$2^{-a}$. Hence $\Phi$ increases by at least $2^{-a}$ at every step, and
$\Phi$ ranges in an interval of length $N$, giving $L\le N2^{a}$.

For a multi-switch step \Cref{lem:switch} gives $\Phi(x_{j+1})>\Phi(x_j)$
just as well, but the estimate compares coordinates of two \emph{different} vectors, of denominators
$D_j$ and $D_{j+1}$; the difference is then at least
$1/(D_jD_{j+1})\ge4^{-a}$, whence $L\le N4^{a}$. Tightness: on $L_n$ one
has $a=n$ and $N=2n+2$, and \Cref{thm:ladder} exhibits a single-switch
improving sequence of length $2^{n}-1=2^{a}-1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The grid bound, vertex by vertex]\label{rem:grid-per-vertex}
The argument refines to each Max vertex separately: if $v$ switches at
rounds $t_1<\dots<t_{n_v}$ of an improving run then, by \Cref{lem:switch},
$\val(v)$ takes at least $n_v+1$ distinct values along the run, each a
rational whose denominator divides an integer at most $2^{a}$
(\Cref{lem:denominator-sharp}) --- different strategies having different
denominators, these lie in the Farey set $\mathcal F_a$ of order $2^{a}$,
of size $\Theta(4^{a})$, and not on one grid --- so
$L\le\sum_vn_v\le m\,(|\mathcal F_a|-1)$, weaker than the theorem's bound
from $a=2$ on, except at $a=0$, where it says that every all-switches run
has length at most $m$: with no average vertex every value is $0$ or $1$
and a vertex switches at most once. That is attained by the ladder with its
average chain deleted, $v_i\to(v_{i+1},t_0)$ for $i<k$ and
$v_k\to(t_1,t_0)$: a stopping one-player game on $N=k+2$ vertices whose
all-switches run from the all-$t_0$ strategy switches $v_k,v_{k-1},\dots,v_1$
one per round --- Howard's rule on a deterministic reachability instance,
folklore --- so that $L=N-2$, the most a one-player stopping game without
average vertices allows. Whether $L\le N-2$ for \emph{every} one-player
stopping game is, by \Cref{rem:ladder}, the open question of Melekopoglou
and Condon for Howard's rule with two actions per state.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
This is the exact form of the ``potential'' barrier. The value vector is
the only monotone quantity available (\Cref{thm:impedance} rules out
potentials of polynomial range), each of its values $\val_\sigma$ lies on
a grid of mesh at least $2^{-a}$, and
that grid is simultaneously an upper bound $N2^{a}$ on every improving
rule and --- by \Cref{thm:ladder} --- essentially attained by a natural
one. So counting arguments of this shape are settled: they cannot prove
less than $2^{a}$ and cannot prove more than $N2^{a}$, and a polynomial
bound must come from somewhere other than the size of the value grid.
Note also that the bound is polynomial exactly when $a=O(\log N)$, which
is the regime already settled by \Cref{thm:few-avg}.
\end{remark}

The ladder settles a more general question at once, and it is worth
recording in the strongest form available, if only because the proof is
one line and the statement is not.

\begin{corollary}[No progress measure has polynomial height]\label{cor:no-height}
Call $\mu$ a \emph{progress measure} if it assigns to every stopping SSG
$G$ a partially ordered set $\Lambda_G$ and a map $\mu_G$ from Max
strategies to $\Lambda_G$ with
\[
  \mu_G(\sigma)\;<\;\mu_G(\sigma[v])\qquad\text{whenever }v\in S_\sigma .
\]
Nothing is assumed about computability, and $\mu_G$ may depend on $G$
arbitrarily. Then $\Lambda_{L_n}$ contains a chain of $2^{n}$ elements, while
$|V(L_n)|=2n+2$. Consequently no polynomial $p$ satisfies
$\operatorname{height}(\Lambda_G)\le p(|V(G)|)$ for every stopping $G$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By \Cref{thm:ladder} the least-index run on $L_n$ visits $2^{n}$ pairwise
distinct strategies, consecutive ones differing by a single strictly
improving switch. Their images under $\mu_{L_n}$ therefore form a chain
of $2^{n}$ elements.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The three statements should not be confused.
\Cref{thm:impedance} rules out potentials of polynomial \emph{range};
\Cref{thm:switch-count} bounds the length of any improving run in terms
of the value grid, and so constrains \emph{rules};
\Cref{cor:no-height} constrains the \emph{measure} instead, and does so
without any hypothesis whatever --- not computability, not monotonicity in
a value, not even that $\Lambda_G$ be a set of numbers. It is an
immediate corollary of \Cref{thm:ladder} rather than a deeper fact, but
it delimits the search sharply: a polynomial bound on strategy
improvement cannot come from exhibiting a short partial order that every
improving switch climbs, because on the ladder every such order is
exponentially tall. What is not excluded, and is exactly what
\Cref{def:missing} asks for, is a measure attached to one particular
\emph{rule}, decreasing only along the switches that rule performs.
\end{remark}



A construction aiming at a long run for a \emph{particular} rule must
therefore arrange for the rule to be given a choice; if it is never given
one, the run is short, whatever the rule.

\begin{proposition}[No serialisation]\label{prop:serialiser}
Let $G$ be stopping and let $\sigma_0,\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_L$ be an
improving run in which $|S_{\sigma_j}|=1$ for every $j<L$ --- so that at
each of those steps every improving rule, single-switch or multi-switch,
performs the same forced flip. Then $L\le d(\sigma_0)\le|\Vmax|$, where
$d$ denotes Hamming distance to a fixed optimal strategy. Consequently,
in an arbitrary improving run, every maximal block of consecutive steps
at which exactly one vertex is strictly switchable has length at most
$|\Vmax|$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Fix $\sigma^\ast\in\Opt$ and let $P:\sigma_0=P_0,P_1,\dots,P_r$ be the
improving path to $\Opt$ supplied by \Cref{thm:short-path}, so that
$r\le d(\sigma_0)\le|\Vmax|$. We claim $P_k=\sigma_k$ for every
$k\le\min(r,L)$. This holds for $k=0$; and if it holds for some
$k<\min(r,L)$ then $|S_{\sigma_k}|=1$, so there is exactly one strictly
improving single flip available at $\sigma_k$, and both $P_{k+1}$ and
$\sigma_{k+1}$ are obtained by it.

Suppose $r<L$. Then $\sigma_r=P_r\in\Opt$, so $S_{\sigma_r}=\emptyset$ by
\Cref{lem:local-global}; but $r<L$ gives $|S_{\sigma_r}|=1$, a
contradiction. Hence $L\le r\le d(\sigma_0)\le|\Vmax|$. For the final
sentence apply this to the strategy that begins the block.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:serialiser}
This constrains every attempt at a superpolynomial lower bound, and it
does so for all improving rules simultaneously. A counter in the
classical shape --- a family whose run visits exponentially many
strategies while each step flips exactly one bit \emph{because that bit
is the only one that may be flipped} --- cannot exist on stopping SSGs.
Any superpolynomially long improving run must offer at least two strictly
switchable vertices at least once in every $|\Vmax|$ consecutive steps,
and the length must come from the \emph{choice} among them rather than
from the forced part of the trajectory. \Cref{thm:ladder} is the extreme
illustration: at $b=0\dots0$ all $n$ vertices of $L_n$ are strictly
switchable, a single well-chosen flip reaches $\Opt$, and the $2^{n}-1$
steps of the least-index run are produced entirely by repeatedly choosing
badly among the available switches. This is \Cref{cor:selection} again,
now visible in the shape a hard instance is forced to have.
\end{remark}


\subsection{Solving the deterministic residue exactly, and why it does not
help}\label{sec:residue}

Every rule considered so far switches Max vertices one at a time or all at
once, and in either case consults the value vector only at the two
successors of the vertex it switches. An obvious way to do better is to
stop switching locally: freeze the average vertices at their current
values, solve the resulting purely deterministic game \emph{exactly}, and
adopt the strategy it prescribes. On the ladder this replaces an
exponential run by a single round. We prove that the acceleration is
sound, that the separation is genuine, and that it is nevertheless
worthless in the worst case, because a value-preserving change of
representation destroys it. The barrier obtained is of a different kind
from the three above: \Cref{thm:impedance}, \Cref{thm:switch-count} and
\Cref{cor:no-height} all limit what a \emph{measure} can achieve, whereas
what is limited here is what extra \emph{graph structure} can achieve.

We first record the fact that makes the deterministic part finite.

\begin{lemma}[Traps characterise stopping]\label{lem:trapchar}
Call $U\subseteq V\setminus\{t_0,t_1\}$ a \emph{trap} if every average
$v\in U$ has both successors in $U$ and every controlled $v\in U$ has at
least one successor in $U$. Then $G$ is stopping if and only if the empty
set is its only trap. Consequently the subgraph of a stopping game
induced on $\Vmax\cup\Vmin$ is acyclic.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $U\ne\emptyset$ be a trap. Choose $(\sigma,\tau)$ selecting, at each
controlled $v\in U$, a successor lying in $U$, and arbitrarily elsewhere.
Then $U$ is closed under $M_{\sigma,\tau}$, so from $u\in U$ the token
never leaves $U$ and in particular never reaches a sink; $G$ is not
stopping.

Conversely let $G$ not be stopping, witnessed by $(\sigma,\tau)$ and $u$
with $\Pr_u[\,\text{absorbed}\,]<1$. In the finite chain
$M_{\sigma,\tau}$ absorption into $\{t_0,t_1\}$ fails from $u$ only if
some recurrent class $R$ reachable from $u$ contains no sink. A recurrent
class is closed: every $v\in R$ has \emph{all} its $M_{\sigma,\tau}$-suc\-ces\-sors
in $R$. For average $v\in R$ those are both successors of $v$; for
controlled $v\in R$ it is the successor chosen by $(\sigma,\tau)$, so at
least one successor of $v$ lies in $R$. Hence $R$ is a nonempty trap.

For the last sentence, a directed cycle $v_0\to v_1\to\dots\to v_{k-1}\to
v_0$ inside $\Vmax\cup\Vmin$ is a trap: it contains no average vertex,
and each of its vertices has a successor on the cycle.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Deterministic residue]\label{def:residue}
Let $G$ be stopping, write $C:=\Vmax\cup\Vmin$, and let
$x\in[0,1]^{\Vavg}$. Extend $x$ to $\Vavg\cup\{t_0,t_1\}$ by $x(t_1):=1$,
$x(t_0):=0$. The \emph{residue} $\widehat G(x)$ is the two-person game
played on $C$ in which the owner of the current vertex moves along one of
its two edges and the play stops as soon as it leaves $C$, with payoff
$x(z)$ at the vertex $z\notin C$ where it stops. By \Cref{lem:trapchar}
the subgraph induced on $C$ is acyclic, so every play stops after at most
$|C|$ moves; the value $D_x$ of $\widehat G(x)$ is therefore given by
backward induction along a reverse topological order of $C$,
\[
  D_x(v)\;=\;
  \begin{cases}
    \max\bigl(D_x(v^{(0)}),D_x(v^{(1)})\bigr), & v\in\Vmax,\\[2pt]
    \min\bigl(D_x(v^{(0)}),D_x(v^{(1)})\bigr), & v\in\Vmin,
  \end{cases}
  \qquad D_x:=x \text{ on } \Vavg\cup\{t_0,t_1\}.
\]
Computing $D_x$, and with it a Max strategy attaining the maximum at
every $v\in\Vmax$, costs $O(N)$ comparisons.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Residue improvement]\label{def:residue-rule}
For a Max strategy $\sigma$ put $x:=\val_\sigma|_{\Vavg}$ and define
$\rho(\sigma)$ by
\[
  \rho(\sigma)(v):=
  \begin{cases}
    \sigma(v) & \text{if } D_x(\sigma(v))=\max\bigl(D_x(v^{(0)}),D_x(v^{(1)})\bigr),\\
    \text{a successor attaining that maximum} & \text{otherwise.}
  \end{cases}
\]
The \emph{residue rule} iterates $\sigma_{k+1}:=\rho(\sigma_k)$ and halts
when $\val_{\sigma_{k+1}}=\val_{\sigma_k}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[The residue rule is correct]\label{thm:residue-correct}
Let $G$ be stopping and $\sigma$ a Max strategy, $x:=\val_\sigma$. Then
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item $D_x\ge x$ pointwise, with equality on $\Vavg\cup\{t_0,t_1\}$;
\item $\val_{\rho(\sigma)}\ \ge\ D_x\ \ge\ \val_\sigma$;
\item $\val_{\rho(\sigma)}=\val_\sigma$ if and only if $\sigma\in\Opt$;
\item consequently the residue rule halts at an optimal strategy after at
      most $N4^{a}$ rounds, each round costing one evaluation of
      $\val_\sigma$ and $O(N)$ further operations.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(a) Equality on $\Vavg\cup\{t_0,t_1\}$ holds by definition. On $C$,
consider the residue $\widehat G(x)$ with Max restricted to $\sigma$. By
\Cref{lem:trapchar} its backward induction has a unique solution, and
$x|_C$ solves it: indeed $T_\sigma x=x$ by
\Cref{thm:stopping-determinacy}, and at $v\in\Vmax$ this reads
$x(v)=x(\sigma(v))$ while at $v\in\Vmin$ it reads
$x(v)=\min(x(v^{(0)}),x(v^{(1)}))$, which are exactly the induction
equations of $\widehat G(x)$ with Max playing $\sigma$. So $x|_C$ is the
value of that restricted game, and $D_x$, being the value when Max plays
optimally, dominates it.

(b) Write $\sigma':=\rho(\sigma)$. We check $T_{\sigma'}D_x\ge D_x$. At
$v\in\Vmax$, $\sigma'(v)$ attains the maximum, so
$(T_{\sigma'}D_x)(v)=D_x(\sigma'(v))=\max(D_x(v^{(0)}),D_x(v^{(1)}))=D_x(v)$.
At $v\in\Vmin$ both sides are $\min(D_x(v^{(0)}),D_x(v^{(1)}))$. At
$c\in\Vavg$, using (a) and then $T_\sigma x=x$,
\[
  (T_{\sigma'}D_x)(c)=\tfrac12\bigl(D_x(c^{(0)})+D_x(c^{(1)})\bigr)
  \;\ge\;\tfrac12\bigl(x(c^{(0)})+x(c^{(1)})\bigr)=x(c)=D_x(c).
\]
Since $G$ is stopping, \Cref{cor:comparison} applied to $T_{\sigma'}$
gives $D_x\le\val_{\sigma'}$, and (a) gives $D_x\ge x=\val_\sigma$.

(c) If $\val_{\sigma'}=\val_\sigma=x$ then (b) squeezes $D_x=x$. Hence at
$v\in\Vmax$, $x(v)=D_x(v)=\max(x(v^{(0)}),x(v^{(1)}))=(Tx)(v)$; at
$v\in\Vmin$ the same holds; and at $c\in\Vavg$ we have
$(Tx)(c)=(T_\sigma x)(c)=x(c)$. So $Tx=x$, and by
\Cref{thm:contraction,thm:stopping-determinacy} $x=w^\ast$, whence
$\sigma\in\Opt$ by \Cref{lem:local-global}. The converse is immediate
from $\val_\sigma=w^\ast\ge\val_{\sigma'}$ together with (b).

(d) By (b) and (c) the vectors $x_k:=\val_{\sigma_k}$ are nondecreasing
and, until the rule halts, differ. By \Cref{lem:denominator-sharp} all
coordinates of $x_k$ share a denominator $d_k\le2^{a}$, so
$\Phi(x_{k+1})-\Phi(x_k)$, where $\Phi(x):=\sum_{u\in V}x(u)\in[0,N]$, is
a positive element of $\frac1{d_kd_{k+1}}\mathbb Z$ and hence at least
$4^{-a}$. Thus there are at most $N4^{a}$ rounds. The per-round cost is
one evaluation of $\val_\sigma$, polynomial by \Cref{thm:eval-stopfree},
plus the $O(N)$ backward induction of \Cref{def:residue}.
\end{proof}

The separation promised above is immediate, and it is as large as
\Cref{thm:ladder} permits.

\begin{proposition}[The residue rule solves the ladder in one
round]\label{prop:residue-ladder}
On $L_n$, started at the strategy $\sigma_0$ taking every first-listed
edge, the residue rule performs exactly one improving round and halts at
the optimum. The least-index and smallest-gap rules take $2^{n}-1$ rounds
from the same start and the all-switches rule takes $n$
(\Cref{thm:ladder}).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Under $\sigma_0$ every $v_i$ moves to $v_{i+1}$, so
$\val_{\sigma_0}(v_i)=0$ for all $i$ because $v_{n+1}=t_0$; consequently
$\val_{\sigma_0}(w_i)=\tfrac12\bigl(\val_{\sigma_0}(v_{i+1})+\val_{\sigma_0}(w_{i+1})\bigr)
=2^{\,i-n-1}$, downwards from $w_{n+1}=t_1$. The residue has
$C=\{v_1,\dots,v_n\}$ with the single chain of edges
$v_i\to v_{i+1}$, so backward induction gives
$D(v_n)=\max\bigl(D(t_0),D(t_1)\bigr)=1$ and then
$D(v_i)=\max\bigl(D(v_{i+1}),x(w_{i+1})\bigr)=\max(1,2^{\,i-n})=1$ for
$i<n$. Hence $\sigma_0(v_i)=v_{i+1}$ already attains the maximum for
$i<n$ and is kept, while $\sigma_0(v_n)=t_0$ has $D=0<1$ and is switched
to $w_{n+1}=t_1$. The resulting strategy is $0\dots01$, which is the
optimum by \Cref{rem:ladder}; the next round changes nothing.
\end{proof}

That separation, however, is an artefact of how the game is drawn. The
following normalisation is value-preserving, cheap, and destroys it
completely --- and, unlike the obvious construction with parallel edges,
it survives every reduction available in \Cref{lem:splice} below.

\begin{lemma}[Bipartite normal form]\label{lem:normalform}
For an SSG $G$ let $N'(G)$ be obtained by replacing each edge $(v,u)$
whose two endpoints are both controlled by
\[
  v\to c_{v,u},\qquad c_{v,u}\to(u,\ d_{v,u}),\qquad d_{v,u}\to(u,\ c_{v,u}),
\]
where $c_{v,u}$ and $d_{v,u}$ are fresh average vertices. Then
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item $|V(N'(G))|\le N+4|\Vmax\cup\Vmin|\le5N$, and in $N'(G)$ no
      controlled vertex has a controlled successor;
\item $N'(G)$ is stopping if and only if $G$ is;
\item $\Vmax(N'(G))=\Vmax(G)$, and under the bijection
      $\sigma\mapsto\tilde\sigma$ sending a controlled successor $u$ to
      $c_{v,u}$ one has $\val^{N'(G)}_{\tilde\sigma}=\val^{G}_\sigma$ on
      $V(G)$ and
      $\val^{N'(G)}_{\tilde\sigma}(c_{v,u})=\val^{N'(G)}_{\tilde\sigma}(d_{v,u})
      =\val^{G}_\sigma(u)$; likewise $\val^{N'(G)}=\val^{G}$ on $V(G)$;
\item $S_{\tilde\sigma}=S_\sigma$ for every $\sigma$, so the all-switches
      runs from $\sigma_0$ in $G$ and from $\tilde\sigma_0$ in $N'(G)$
      have the same length and pass through corresponding strategies;
\item none of the new vertices is a redex of either reduction of
      \Cref{lem:splice}; in particular $N'(G)$ carries no redex at all if
      $G$ carries none.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(i) is immediate; at most $2|\Vmax\cup\Vmin|$ edges are replaced and each
costs two vertices.

(iii) For $y\in[0,1]^{V(G)}$ let $\hat y$ extend $y$ by
$\hat y(c_{v,u}):=\hat y(d_{v,u}):=y(u)$. Then $T^{N'(G)}\hat y$ is the
extension of $T^{G}y$: at $c_{v,u}$ the average of $y(u)$ and
$\hat y(d_{v,u})=y(u)$ is $y(u)$, and symmetrically at $d_{v,u}$; at a
controlled $v$ the maximum or minimum is taken over the same two numbers
as before; elsewhere nothing changed. Conversely, if $z$ is a fixed point
of $T^{N'(G)}$ then subtracting the equations at $c_{v,u}$ and
$d_{v,u}$ gives $z(c_{v,u})-z(d_{v,u})=\frac12(z(d_{v,u})-z(c_{v,u}))$,
so $z(c_{v,u})=z(d_{v,u})=z(u)$ and $z$ is of the form $\hat y$. The
fixed points therefore correspond bijectively, and the same computation
applies to $T_\sigma$ and $T_{\tilde\sigma}$. For stopping games these
fixed points are unique (\Cref{thm:contraction}); in general the
bijection preserves pre-fixed points in both directions, hence least
fixed points, and one applies \Cref{thm:lfp-general}.

(ii) Let $U$ be a nonempty trap of $N'(G)$. If $c_{v,u}\in U$ then both
its successors lie in $U$, so $u\in U$; the same holds for $d_{v,u}$. A
controlled $v\in U$ has some successor in $U$, necessarily some
$c_{v,u}$, hence some successor $u$ of $v$ in $G$ lies in $U$; an old
average vertex of $U$ has both its (unchanged) successors in $U$. So
$U\cap V(G)$ is a trap of $G$, and it is nonempty because every new
vertex of $U$ forces the old vertex $u$ into $U$, and $u$ is not a sink.
Conversely a trap $U$ of $G$ lifts to
$U\cup\{c_{v,u},d_{v,u}:u\in U\}$. Apply \Cref{lem:trapchar}.

(iv) By (iii), $\val_{\tilde\sigma}(c_{v,u})=\val_\sigma(u)$, so the test
defining $S_{\tilde\sigma}$ at $v$ compares the same two numbers as the
test defining $S_\sigma$. All-switches replaces $\sigma$ by
$\sigma[S_\sigma]$, and the correspondence commutes with that.

(v) A splice redex is an average vertex with two equal successors;
$c_{v,u}$ has successors $u$ and $d_{v,u}$, which are distinct because
$d_{v,u}$ is new, and symmetrically for $d_{v,u}$. A merge redex is a
pair of average vertices with equal successor multisets; the multiset of
$c_{v,u}$ contains $d_{v,u}$, which is a successor of no other vertex
than $c_{v,u}$, and symmetrically. Old average vertices keep their old
successors, so no old pair becomes a redex either.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Residue-blind rule]\label{def:residue-blind}
A rule $R$ assigning to every stopping SSG $G$ and Max strategy $\sigma$
a strategy $R(G,\sigma)$ is \emph{residue-blind} if
$R(G,\sigma)=\sigma[S_\sigma]$ whenever no controlled vertex of $G$ has a
controlled successor.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Normalisation barrier]\label{thm:normalform-barrier}
The residue rule is residue-blind. If $R$ is residue-blind and halts
within $p(|V(G)|)$ rounds on every stopping SSG $G$ and every starting
strategy, with $p$ nondecreasing, then the all-switches rule halts within
$p(5N)$ rounds on every
stopping SSG with $N$ vertices. In particular no residue-blind rule runs
in polynomially many rounds unless the all-switches rule already does,
and any superpolynomial lower bound for all-switches transfers verbatim
to every residue-blind rule.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If no controlled vertex of $G$ has a controlled successor then
$\Vmax\cup\Vmin$ carries no edges, so \Cref{def:residue} gives
$D_x(v)=\max\bigl(x(v^{(0)}),x(v^{(1)})\bigr)$ for $v\in\Vmax$ with
$x=\val_\sigma$; hence $\rho(\sigma)(v)\ne\sigma(v)$ exactly when
$\val_\sigma(\bar\sigma(v))>\val_\sigma(\sigma(v))$, that is exactly when
$v\in S_\sigma$. So $\rho(\sigma)=\sigma[S_\sigma]$ and the residue rule
is residue-blind.

Now let $G$ be stopping with $N$ vertices and let $\sigma_0$ be any
starting strategy. By \Cref{lem:normalform}(i),(ii) the game $N'(G)$ is
stopping with at most $5N$ vertices and no controlled vertex has a
controlled successor, so the run of $R$ on $(N'(G),\tilde\sigma_0)$ is
\emph{by definition} the all-switches run there; by hypothesis it halts
within $p(5N)$ rounds. By \Cref{lem:normalform}(iv) the all-switches run
on $(G,\sigma_0)$ has the same length.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:normalform}
The two statements should be read together.
\Cref{prop:residue-ladder} is a genuine exponential separation: on
$L_n$ the residue rule needs one round where the least-index rule needs
$2^{n}-1$, and it obtains it by using the structure of the graph
\emph{between} controlled vertices, which no switching rule in
\Cref{def:rule} inspects. \Cref{thm:normalform-barrier} says that all of
that structure can be deleted at a cost of at most $4N$ extra average
vertices, without changing a single value, and without leaving anything a
preprocessing step could recognise and undo --- that is the point of
\Cref{lem:normalform}(v), and it is why the gadget is a two-cycle rather
than the obvious pair of parallel edges. What the residue rule exploits
is therefore not a property of the game but a property of the
presentation, and the same applies to any rule that resolves the
deterministic part optimally --- by attractor sweeps, by condensation into
strongly connected components, or by any other means. This is why the
barrier is stated for the whole class \Cref{def:residue-blind} rather
than for one algorithm. It also isolates what a successful rule must do:
it must remain fast on games in which $\Vmax\cup\Vmin$ is an independent
set, so that the only structure left to exploit is the interaction of the
average vertices --- which is precisely where \Cref{thm:switch-count}
places the difficulty, and precisely what \Cref{thm:matching-barrier} and
\Cref{thm:slack-barrier} show to be hard for comparison mechanisms as
well.
\end{remark}

The normalisation raises $a$, and one may ask whether the parameter is
therefore worthless as a measure of difficulty. It is, and the reductions
that would repair it do not go far enough.

\begin{lemma}[Two reductions of the average set]\label{lem:splice}
Let $G$ be a stopping SSG.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item \textup{(Splice)} If $c\in\Vavg$ has $c^{(0)}=c^{(1)}=u$, let $G/c$
      be obtained by deleting $c$ and redirecting every edge into $c$ to
      $u$. Then $u\ne c$, $G/c$ is stopping, and
      $\val_{G/c}=\val_G$ on $V\setminus\{c\}$ while
      $\val_G(c)=\val_G(u)$. The same holds for $\val_\sigma$, for every
      $\sigma$.
\item \textup{(Merge)} If $c,d\in\Vavg$ are distinct and
      $\{c^{(0)},c^{(1)}\}=\{d^{(0)},d^{(1)}\}$ as multisets, the game
      obtained by deleting $d$ and redirecting every edge into $d$ to $c$
      is stopping and has the same values, with $\val(d)=\val(c)$.
\end{enumerate}
Each reduction lowers $|\Vavg|$ by one, so at most $a$ of them apply in
succession.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) If $u=c$ then $\{c\}$ is a trap and $G$ is not stopping by
\Cref{lem:trapchar}. For $y\in[0,1]^{V\setminus\{c\}}$ let $\hat y$ be its
extension by $\hat y(c):=y(u)$. Then $\hat y$ is a fixed point of $T_G$
if and only if $y$ is a fixed point of $T_{G/c}$: at $c$ the equation
$\hat y(c)=\frac12(\hat y(u)+\hat y(u))$ holds automatically, at every
other vertex the two operators read the same because an occurrence of
$c$ contributes $\hat y(c)=y(u)$ on one side and the redirected
occurrence of $u$ contributes $y(u)$ on the other. Traps correspond in
the same way ($c$ belongs to a trap iff $u$ does), so $G/c$ is stopping
by \Cref{lem:trapchar}, and the fixed points are unique by
\Cref{thm:contraction}. The argument is unchanged with $T$ replaced by
$T_\sigma$.

(b) Identical, with $\hat y(d):=y(c)$: the defining equation at $d$
coincides with the one at $c$ because the two vertices have the same
successor multiset.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[$a$ is a parameter of the presentation]\label{prop:a-presentation}
Let $G$ be a stopping SSG carrying no redex of \Cref{lem:splice} and
with at least one edge between two average vertices. Applying the gadget
of \Cref{lem:normalform} to such an edge
--- replacing $c_0\to u$ by $c_0\to c$, $c\to(u,d)$, $d\to(u,c)$ ---
yields a stopping SSG with the same values on $V(G)$, with two more
average vertices, and with no redex of \Cref{lem:splice} among the new
vertices. Iterating produces, for every $M$, a stopping SSG carrying no
redex at all, with the same value at every vertex of $G$, and with
$a>M$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Values, stopping and irreducibility are proved exactly as in
\Cref{lem:normalform}(ii),(iii),(v); the computation there never used
that $c_0$ was controlled. Each application adds two average vertices and
creates a new average-to-average edge $c\to u$ if $u$ is average, and
$c_0\to c$ in any case, so the construction may be repeated
indefinitely. The hypothesis that $G$ itself carries no redex cannot be
dropped: two average vertices with equal successor multisets and no
average successor --- $0,1\to(t_0,t_1)$ beside $2\to(3,t_1)$,
$3\to(t_0,t_1)$ --- form a merge redex that no application of the gadget
touches.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:parameter-presentation}
\Cref{lem:splice} lowers $a$ where it can, but \Cref{prop:a-presentation}
shows it cannot canonicalise it: no exhaustively reduced representative
is unique, and $a$ may be inflated without bound while every value is
preserved. So $\mathrm{poly}(N)2^{a}$ in \Cref{thm:few-avg}, and $N2^{a}$
in \Cref{thm:switch-count}, are statements about the input as presented,
and $a$ cannot certify hardness --- for the same reason as
\Cref{rem:owner-blind}, and by an even cruder mechanism. The observation
also settles a natural hope: one cannot reduce $a$ to $O(\log N)$ by
massaging the presentation, because the presentation carries no
$a$-minimising normal form to massage it into. We verified the
construction in exact arithmetic on the five-vertex game of
\Cref{prop:freeze-escapes}, where three rounds of it raise $a$ from $3$
to $9$, $39$ and $189$ with every value unchanged and no redex at any
stage.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Exact scope of the barrier]\label{rem:barrier-scope}
The obvious objection to \Cref{thm:normalform-barrier} is that a rule
might begin by simplifying the game it is handed. That objection is
exactly what \Cref{lem:normalform}(v) answers: if $G$ carries no redex
then neither does $N'(G)$, so neither reduction of \Cref{lem:splice}
fires, and the padding cannot be recognised and removed by them. This is
the reason for the two-cycle gadget; the shorter construction that
replaces $v\to u$ by $v\to c$, $c\to(u,u)$ has all the other properties
and is undone by a single splice.

What is not claimed is a lower bound. \Cref{thm:normalform-barrier}
transfers a superpolynomial bound for all-switches to every
residue-blind rule, and no such bound is proved in this document; in the
other direction it says only that the residue rule cannot be the missing
mechanism unless all-switches already is.
\end{remark}

Finally we delimit the barrier from above, because the delimitation is
sharp and it exhibits sound improving rules that fall outside
\Cref{def:rule} altogether. The residue rule freezes \emph{all} average
vertices; one may instead free some of them.

\begin{definition}[Freezing rule]\label{def:freeze}
Let $B\subseteq\Vavg$. Given $\sigma$, put $x:=\val_\sigma$ and let
$\widehat G_B(x)$ be the game played on $B\cup\Vmax\cup\Vmin$ obtained
from $G$ by declaring every average vertex outside $B$ a terminal with
payoff $x$. Let $\rho_B(\sigma)$ be an optimal Max strategy in
$\widehat G_B(x)$, chosen to agree with $\sigma$ wherever $\sigma$
already attains the optimum. The rule $\rho_\emptyset$ is the residue
rule of \Cref{def:residue-rule}, and $\rho_{\Vavg}$ solves $G$ outright.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Freezing rules are sound]\label{prop:freeze-sound}
Let $G$ be stopping and $B\subseteq\Vavg$. Then $\widehat G_B(x)$ is a
stopping game with terminal payoffs, and with $D$ its value extended by
$x$ off $B\cup\Vmax\cup\Vmin$,
\[
  \val_{\rho_B(\sigma)}\ \ge\ D\ \ge\ \val_\sigma,
\]
with $\val_{\rho_B(\sigma)}=\val_\sigma$ if and only if $\sigma\in\Opt$.
The iteration therefore halts at an optimal strategy after at most
$N4^{a}$ rounds. For $|B|=1$ one round costs one evaluation of
$\val_\sigma$ and $O(N^{2})$ further arithmetic operations.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A trap of $\widehat G_B(x)$ avoids all terminals, in particular all
average vertices outside $B$, so it is a trap of $G$; by
\Cref{lem:trapchar} it is empty, and $\widehat G_B(x)$ is stopping. Note
that \Cref{lem:absorption} and \Cref{thm:contraction} apply to a game
with terminal payoffs verbatim: their proofs use only that the terminals
are absorbing and never that they carry the payoffs $0$ and $1$. So the
operator of $\widehat G_B(x)$, and the operator obtained from it by
fixing either player's strategy, each have exactly one fixed point, and
every orbit converges to it; this is used twice below.

The three displayed relations are proved exactly as in
\Cref{thm:residue-correct}. For $D\ge\val_\sigma$: $T_\sigma x=x$ says
that $x$, restricted to $B\cup\Vmax\cup\Vmin$, satisfies the defining
equations of $\widehat G_B(x)$ with Max playing $\sigma$, and those have
a unique solution because the game is stopping; so $x$ is its value under
$\sigma$, which the optimal value $D$ dominates. For
$T_{\rho_B(\sigma)}D\ge D$: at controlled vertices and at vertices of $B$
one has equality, and at an average $c\notin B$,
$(T_{\rho_B(\sigma)}D)(c)=\frac12(D(c^{(0)})+D(c^{(1)}))\ge
\frac12(x(c^{(0)})+x(c^{(1)}))=x(c)=D(c)$; now apply
\Cref{cor:comparison}. If $\val_{\rho_B(\sigma)}=\val_\sigma$ then
$D=x$, whence $Tx=x$ as in \Cref{thm:residue-correct}(c) and
$\sigma\in\Opt$ by \Cref{lem:local-global}.

For the round bound, the value vectors strictly increase, and by
\Cref{lem:denominator-sharp} consecutive ones have denominators at most
$2^{a}$, so $\Phi(x):=\sum_{u}x(u)\in[0,N]$ rises by at least $4^{-a}$
each round.

For $B=\{c\}$: for $y\in[0,1]$ let $D_y$ be the value of the game with
$c$ frozen at $y$ as well. By \Cref{lem:trapchar} the controlled
subgraph is acyclic, so $D_y$ is computed by backward induction, and an
induction along that order shows each $D_y(v)$ is nondecreasing,
continuous and piecewise affine in $y$ with slopes in $\{0,1\}$ and
breakpoints inside the set $S$ of frozen values together with $\{0,1\}$;
a maximum or minimum of two such functions is again one, and two of them
cross only where a constant meets $y$. Hence
$F(y):=\frac12\bigl(D_y(c^{(0)})+D_y(c^{(1)})\bigr)$ is piecewise affine
with at most $N+1$ pieces, and $(D_y,y)$ is a fixed point of the residual
operator exactly when $F(y)=y$. Uniqueness of that fixed point gives a
unique solution, found by solving one affine equation on each piece.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[One freed average vertex escapes the
barrier]\label{prop:freeze-escapes}
Let $G^{\dagger}$ be the SSG on vertices $0,\dots,4$ with $t_0=5$,
$t_1=6$, $\Vmax=\{2,3\}$, $\Vmin=\emptyset$, $\Vavg=\{0,1,4\}$ and
\[
  0\to(0,3),\quad 1\to(2,0),\quad 2\to(4,1),\quad 3\to(t_0,t_1),\quad
  4\to(1,3).
\]
Then $G^{\dagger}$ is stopping, carries no redex of \Cref{lem:splice},
and no controlled vertex of it has a controlled successor. At
$\sigma=(2\mapsto1,\ 3\mapsto t_0)$ one has $\val_\sigma\equiv0$ and
$S_\sigma=\{3\}$, whereas $\rho_{\{4\}}(\sigma)$ switches both $2$ and
$3$. Consequently $\rho_{\{4\}}$ is neither residue-blind nor a switching
rule in the sense of \Cref{def:rule}, and
\Cref{thm:normalform-barrier} does not apply to it.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
No average vertex has two equal successors and no two have the same
successor multiset, so there is no redex; vertex $2$ points to the
average vertices $4$ and $1$ and vertex $3$ to the two sinks, so the
controlled vertices are independent; and a trap would have to contain
$3$, which is impossible since both successors of $3$ are sinks, hence
also cannot contain $0$, $4$ or $2$, hence not $1$, so $G^{\dagger}$ is
stopping by \Cref{lem:trapchar}.

Under $\sigma$, vertex $3$ pays $0$; then $\val_\sigma(0)=
\frac12(\val_\sigma(0)+0)$ forces $\val_\sigma(0)=0$, and
$\val_\sigma(1)=\frac12(\val_\sigma(2)+0)=\frac12\val_\sigma(1)$ forces
$\val_\sigma(1)=0$, so $\val_\sigma\equiv0$. At vertex $2$ the
alternative successor $4$ also has value $0$, so $2\notin S_\sigma$,
while at $3$ the alternative is $t_1$; thus $S_\sigma=\{3\}$.

Freeing $4$ and freezing $0$ and $1$ at $0$, the residual reads
$\val(3)=\max(0,1)=1$, $\val(4)=\frac12(x(1)+\val(3))=\frac12$ and
$\val(2)=\max(\val(4),x(1))=\frac12$, attained at the successor $4$. So
the optimal residual strategy sends $2$ to $4$ and $3$ to $t_1$, and
$\rho_{\{4\}}(\sigma)$ switches both. Finally $w^\ast\equiv1$: with
$3\mapsto t_1$ one gets $w^\ast(3)=1$, then $w^\ast(0)=\frac12(w^\ast(0)+1)=1$,
$w^\ast(4)=\frac12(w^\ast(1)+1)$, $w^\ast(2)=\max(w^\ast(4),w^\ast(1))$ and
$w^\ast(1)=\frac12(w^\ast(2)+1)$, whose unique solution is $1$ throughout.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:freeze}
Three consequences, and one caution.
First, \Cref{thm:normalform-barrier} is exactly a statement about the
bottom level $B=\emptyset$ of \Cref{def:freeze}: already
$|B|=1$ escapes it, on a game that is reduced and normal --- though
\Cref{thm:window-barrier} below shows that the escape is itself a
property of the presentation, and that a longer padding restores the
barrier at every bounded level of \Cref{def:freeze} at once. The escape is
measurable but small on the instances we can compute: on the normalised
ladders $N'(L_n)$, $n=2,\dots,6$, the rule that tries every single $c$ and
keeps the best takes $n-1$ rounds where all-switches and the residue rule
both take $n$. On the ladder itself the residue rule takes one round
(\Cref{prop:residue-ladder}); it is the normalisation that costs it, and
freeing one average vertex buys back exactly one round of what was lost.
Second, \Cref{prop:freeze-escapes} shows that the sound improving rules
form a strictly larger class than \Cref{def:rule}: a rule may switch a
vertex that is not strictly switchable and still never decrease a value.
So \Cref{thm:switch-count}, \Cref{cor:no-height} and
\Cref{prop:serialiser}, all of which quantify over runs of strictly
improving flips, do not constrain $\rho_B$ --- though the counting
argument itself survives, since the proof of \Cref{thm:switch-count}'s
multi-switch bound uses only that the value vectors increase strictly,
and that holds for every $\rho_B$ by \Cref{prop:freeze-sound}.
Third, this locates precisely what the normalisation removes: it deletes
all structure among the controlled vertices and none among the average
ones, and it is the latter that $\rho_B$ exploits.
The caution is that nothing here is progress on \Cref{prob:main}. No
bound better than $N4^{a}$ is proved for any $\rho_B$ with $B\ne\Vavg$,
and $\rho_{\Vavg}$, which finishes in one round, is not polynomial time.
\end{remark}

The escape of \Cref{prop:freeze-escapes} is real, but it is not what it
looks like. A longer padding removes it, and removes it at every level of
\Cref{def:freeze} at once.

\begin{definition}[The $k$-blinding normalisation]\label{def:kblind}
Let $k\ge0$ and put $m:=k+1$. For an SSG $G$ let $N_k(G)$ be obtained by
replacing \emph{every} edge $(z,u)$ of $G$ --- $z$ any non-sink, $u$
either of its two successors --- by a chain of $m$ copies of the gadget
of \Cref{lem:normalform}:
\[
  z\to g^{z,u}_1,\qquad
  g^{z,u}_j\to\bigl(g^{z,u}_{j+1},\,h^{z,u}_j\bigr),\qquad
  h^{z,u}_j\to\bigl(g^{z,u}_{j+1},\,g^{z,u}_j\bigr)
  \qquad(1\le j\le m),
\]
where $g^{z,u}_{m+1}:=u$ and the $2m$ vertices $g^{z,u}_j,h^{z,u}_j$ are
fresh average vertices, one family for each edge. We call
$g^{z,u}_1,\dots,g^{z,u}_m$ the \emph{spine} of that edge. Thus
$N_0(G)$ subdivides every edge once, where the $N'(G)$ of
\Cref{lem:normalform} subdivides only the edges joining two controlled
vertices.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:kblind}
Let $G$ be an SSG on $N$ vertices and put $H:=N_k(G)$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item $|V(H)|=N+4(k+1)(N-2)\le(4k+5)N$; no controlled vertex of $H$ has
      a controlled successor; and $H$ carries no redex of either
      reduction of \Cref{lem:splice} at all, whether or not $G$ carries
      one.
\item For $y\in[0,1]^{V(G)}$ let $\hat y$ agree with $y$ on $V(G)$ and
      take the value $y(u)$ at every vertex of the chain of the edge
      $(z,u)$. Then $T^{H}\hat y=\widehat{T^{G}y}$, and every fixed point
      of $T^{H}$ has the form $\hat y$; the same holds for $T_\sigma$
      under the bijection $\sigma\mapsto\tilde\sigma$ sending the
      successor $u$ of $v$ to $g^{v,u}_1$.
\item $H$ is stopping if and only if $G$ is, and then
      $\val^{H}=\widehat{\val^{G}}$ and
      $\val^{H}_{\tilde\sigma}=\widehat{\val^{G}_{\sigma}}$ for every
      $\sigma$.
\item $S_{\tilde\sigma}=S_\sigma$ for every $\sigma$, so the all-switches
      runs from $\sigma_0$ in $G$ and from $\tilde\sigma_0$ in $H$ have
      the same length and pass through corresponding strategies.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(i) Each of the $2(N-2)$ edges contributes $2m=2(k+1)$ vertices, and
every successor of a controlled vertex is a spine head, which is
average. For the redexes, note first that an old average vertex now has
as its two successors the spine heads of its own two edges, which are
distinct fresh vertices even when its two $G$-successors coincided; and
that distinct old vertices have disjoint families of spine heads. A
splice redex is an average vertex with two equal successors: no old
vertex is one by the previous sentence, and $g^{z,u}_j$ has the distinct
successors $g^{z,u}_{j+1}$ and $h^{z,u}_j$, while $h^{z,u}_j$ has
$g^{z,u}_{j+1}$ and $g^{z,u}_j$. A merge redex is a pair of distinct
average vertices with equal successor multisets. If a vertex $v$ has a
successor $w$ of which $v$ is the unique predecessor, no other vertex can
have $v$'s multiset, since it would then be a second predecessor of $w$.
Every $g^{z,u}_j$ has such a successor, namely $h^{z,u}_j$. An old
average vertex $z$ has the multiset $\{g^{z,u_0}_1,g^{z,u_1}_1\}$ of its
two spine heads, and the only other predecessor of a spine head
$g^{z,u}_1$ is $h^{z,u}_1$, whose multiset $\{g^{z,u}_2,g^{z,u}_1\}$
contains a vertex outside $z$'s; so no vertex shares $z$'s multiset.
There remain the pairs $h^{z,u}_j,h^{z',u'}_{j'}$, whose
multisets $\{g^{z,u}_{j+1},g^{z,u}_j\}$ and
$\{g^{z',u'}_{j'+1},g^{z',u'}_{j'}\}$ are equal only if the chains and
the indices coincide. So $H$ carries no redex.

(ii) The two equations at $g_j$ and $h_j$ read
$2y(g_j)=y(g_{j+1})+y(h_j)$ and $2y(h_j)=y(g_{j+1})+y(g_j)$; subtracting
gives $3\bigl(y(g_j)-y(h_j)\bigr)=0$, so $y(g_j)=y(h_j)=y(g_{j+1})$, and
descending induction on $j$ gives $y\equiv y(u)$ on the whole chain.
This shows both that $\hat y$ satisfies the new equations wherever $y$
satisfied the old ones and that every fixed point is of that form; at a
controlled or old average vertex the two operators take the maximum,
minimum or mean of the same two numbers, because a spine head carries
the value of the head of its edge.

(iii) Let $U$ be a nonempty trap of $H$. If $g^{z,u}_j\in U$ then both
its successors lie in $U$, hence $g^{z,u}_{j+1}\in U$ and, descending,
$u\in U$; the same holds for $h^{z,u}_j$. Since a trap contains no sink,
this forces $u$ to be a non-sink vertex of $G$, so $U\cap V(G)\ne
\emptyset$. A controlled $v\in U$ has some successor $g^{v,u}_1\in U$
and hence some $G$-successor $u$ in $U$; an old average vertex of $U$ has
both spine heads in $U$ and hence both $G$-successors in $U$. So
$U\cap V(G)$ is a nonempty trap of $G$. Conversely a nonempty trap of
$G$ lifts by adjoining the chains of all its internal edges. Apply
\Cref{lem:trapchar}, and then (ii) with \Cref{thm:contraction} for the
values.

(iv) By (iii) $\val^{H}_{\tilde\sigma}(g^{v,u}_1)=\val^{G}_\sigma(u)$, so
the test defining $S_{\tilde\sigma}$ at $v$ compares the same two numbers
as the test defining $S_\sigma$; and the correspondence commutes with
$\sigma\mapsto\sigma[S_\sigma]$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[$k$-window rule]\label{def:window}
A rule $R$ assigning to every stopping SSG $G$ and Max strategy $\sigma$
a strategy $R(G,\sigma)$ is a \emph{$k$-window rule} if for every such
pair there is a set $B=B_R(G,\sigma)\subseteq\Vavg$ with $|B|\le k$ and
$R(G,\sigma)=\rho_B(\sigma)$ in the sense of \Cref{def:freeze}. The
choice of $B$ is unrestricted: it may depend on $G$ and on $\sigma$ in
any way whatever and need not be computable. In particular the rule that
evaluates $\rho_B(\sigma)$ for all $\binom{a}{\le k}$ admissible $B$ and
returns the one maximising $\val_{\rho_B(\sigma)}$ is a $k$-window rule,
and so is the residue rule, with $k=0$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[The freezing hierarchy is blinded]\label{thm:window-barrier}
Let $G$ be a stopping SSG, let $k\ge0$ and put $H:=N_k(G)$. Then for
every Max strategy $\tilde\sigma$ of $H$ and every $B\subseteq\Vavg(H)$
with $|B|\le k$,
\[
  \rho_B(\tilde\sigma)\;=\;\tilde\sigma[S_{\tilde\sigma}].
\]
