Consequently, if a $k$-window rule halts within $p(|V|)$ rounds on every
stopping SSG and every starting strategy, with $p$ nondecreasing, then
the all-switches rule halts within $p\bigl((4k+5)N\bigr)$ rounds on
every stopping SSG with $N$ vertices. In particular, let $\kappa$ be a
nondecreasing window function with $\kappa(N)\le cN^{\alpha}$ for
constants $c$ and $\alpha<1$, and call $R$ a $\kappa$-window rule if on
every game with $N$ vertices it is a $\kappa(N)$-window rule. Then no
$\kappa$-window rule runs in polynomially many rounds unless the
all-switches rule already does, and any superpolynomial lower bound for
all-switches transfers to every such rule. Windows proportional to $N$
are \emph{not} covered: $\rho_{\Vavg}$ is an $a$-window rule with
$a\le N-2$ and halts in one round.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $x:=\val^{H}_{\tilde\sigma}$ and let $D$ be the value of the
residual $\widehat H_B(x)$ of \Cref{def:freeze}, extended by $x$ off
$B\cup\Vmax\cup\Vmin$. Fix an edge $(z,u)$ of $G$ and abbreviate its
chain by $g_1,\dots,g_m,h_1,\dots,h_m$. By \Cref{lem:kblind}(ii),
$x$ is constant on that chain, equal to the number $\alpha:=x(u)$.

Since $|B|\le k<m$, at least one spine vertex lies outside $B$; let $j$
be least with $g_j\notin B$. Put
\[
  W\;:=\;\{g_1,\dots,g_{j-1}\}\ \cup\ \bigl(\{h_1,\dots,h_{j-1}\}\cap B\bigr),
\]
a set of average vertices all of which lie in $B$ and are therefore
vertices --- not terminals --- of $\widehat H_B(x)$. Every successor of a
vertex of $W$ either lies in $W$ or is a terminal of $\widehat H_B(x)$
belonging to the same chain: the successors of $g_i$ with $i<j$ are
$g_{i+1}$, which lies in $W$ if $i+1<j$ and is the terminal $g_j$ if
$i+1=j$, and $h_i$, which lies in $W$ if $h_i\in B$ and is a terminal
otherwise; the successors of an $h_i\in W$ are $g_{i+1}$ and $g_i$,
handled the same way. Each such terminal carries the payoff $\alpha$,
because $x$ is constant on the chain. The equations of
$\widehat H_B(x)$ on $W$ therefore form a linear system in the unknowns
$(D(w))_{w\in W}$ whose only inhomogeneous data is $\alpha$, and that
system is nonsingular because $\widehat H_B(x)$ is stopping
(\Cref{prop:freeze-sound}). The constant vector $\alpha$ solves it, so
$D\equiv\alpha$ on $W$. If $j=1$ then $g_1\notin B$ is itself a terminal
with payoff $x(g_1)=\alpha$. Either way
\[
  D(g_1)\;=\;\alpha\;=\;x(g_1),
\]
and this holds for every edge of $G$, the argument being confined to a
single chain.

Now let $v$ be a controlled vertex of $H$. Its two successors are the
spine heads $g^{v,v^{(0)}}_1$ and $g^{v,v^{(1)}}_1$, at which $D$ agrees
with $x$ by the display; so for $v\in\Vmax$
\[
  D(v)=\max\bigl(D(g^{v,v^{(0)}}_1),D(g^{v,v^{(1)}}_1)\bigr)
      =\max\bigl(x(g^{v,v^{(0)}}_1),x(g^{v,v^{(1)}}_1)\bigr).
\]
The maximum is attained at $\tilde\sigma(v)$ exactly when
$v\notin S_{\tilde\sigma}$, and is attained \emph{only} at the other
successor when $v\in S_{\tilde\sigma}$. By the tie-break of
\Cref{def:freeze}, $\rho_B(\tilde\sigma)$ keeps $\tilde\sigma(v)$ in the
first case and switches in the second, which is
$\tilde\sigma[S_{\tilde\sigma}]$.

For the transfer, let $R$ be a $k$-window rule and $\sigma_0$ any
starting strategy of a stopping $G$ on $N$ vertices. By
\Cref{lem:kblind}(i),(iii) the game $H$ is stopping with at most
$(4k+5)N$ vertices, and by the first part every round of $R$ on
$(H,\tilde\sigma_0)$ is an all-switches round, so the run of $R$ there is
the all-switches run and halts within $p((4k+5)N)$ rounds. By
\Cref{lem:kblind}(iv) the all-switches run on $(G,\sigma_0)$ has the same
length.

For a window function $\kappa$ the padding must outgrow the window
\emph{on the padded game}: given $G$ on $N$ vertices take the least $k$
with $\kappa((4k+5)N)\le k$. It exists and is polynomial in $N$, since
$c((4k+5)N)^{\alpha}\le c(9kN)^{\alpha}\le k$ as soon as
$k\ge c^{1/(1-\alpha)}(9N)^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}$. On $H=N_k(G)$ the rule
uses windows of size at most $\kappa(|V(H)|)\le\kappa((4k+5)N)\le k$, so
the first part applies and the transfer goes through with the polynomial
$p((4k+5)N)$. For $\kappa(N)=\Theta(N)$ no such $k$ exists, which is why
the linear case is excluded.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:window-barrier}
\Cref{prop:freeze-escapes} and \Cref{thm:window-barrier} must be read
together, and the second is the stronger statement. Freeing one average
vertex does escape \Cref{thm:normalform-barrier}, and it does so on a
game that is both reduced and normal; but the escape is again a property
of the presentation. \Cref{def:kblind} places every value in the game
behind a chain of $k+1$ average two-cycles, so that an improvement
originating at a strictly switchable controlled vertex can be seen at
another controlled vertex only by a residual that keeps a whole spine
live --- and a window of $k$ vertices cannot. The length $k+1$ is the
least that works: if the whole spine $g_1,\dots,g_m$ of an edge $(z,u)$
lies in $B$ and $D(u)>x(u)$, then $D(g_m)=\frac12(D(u)+D(h_m))
\ge\frac12(D(u)+x(u))>x(u)$ because $D\ge x$ by
\Cref{prop:freeze-sound}, and descending induction gives $D(g_j)>x(g_j)$
for every $j$, so a window of size $k+1$ does carry a strict improvement
across a spine. It can also change the verdict. Let $G$ have $\Vmax=\{p,q,r\}$ and
$\Vavg=\{c\}$ with $p\to(t_1,c)$, $c\to(t_0,p)$, $q\to(p,c)$ and
$r\to(q,c)$; in $N_1(G)$ take $\tilde\sigma$ sending $p$ into the spine
of $(p,t_1)$ and $q,r$ into the spines of $(q,c)$ and $(r,c)$, so that
$S_{\tilde\sigma}=\{q\}$; with $B$ the two-vertex spine of the edge
$(r,q)$, $\rho_B(\tilde\sigma)$ switches $r$ as well as $q$, since the
window carries $q$'s improvement to $r$ within the round. (Verified in
exact arithmetic; over all eight strategies and eight spines of that game
three such pairs occur.)

Two limits are worth stating plainly. The transfer is useful only while
$(4k+5)N$ is polynomial in $N$, and the padding must outgrow the rule's
window on the padded game; that is why the theorem covers window
functions $\kappa(N)=O(N^{\alpha})$ with $\alpha<1$ and not linear ones.
And the theorem says nothing about windows that are a constant fraction
of $\Vavg$, in particular nothing about $\rho_{\Vavg}$, which finishes in
one round and is not polynomial: on $N_k(G)$ the average set has grown to
$a+4(k+1)(N-2)$, so a proportional window is not blinded by this
construction and we do not know whether it can be. What the barrier says is therefore this: no rule that solves exactly
$O(N^{\alpha})$ average vertices, for any fixed $\alpha<1$, can be the
missing mechanism unless the all-switches rule already is; rules that
solve a constant fraction of them, $\rho_{\Vavg}$ among them, are outside
its reach.
\end{remark}

Every rule considered so far moves one player. Moving both at once, and
letting each veto the other, gives a rule that needs no fallback clause at
all.

\begin{definition}[Bidirectional improvement]\label{def:bsi}
Let $G$ be stopping and $(\sigma,\tau)$ a pair of positional strategies.
Write $L:=\val_\sigma$, the value when Max is frozen to $\sigma$, and
$U:=\val^\tau$, the value when Min is frozen to $\tau$; both are computable
by \Cref{thm:eval-stopfree} applied to $G$ and to the dual $\bar G$ of
\Cref{lem:duality}. Put
\[
  S_\sigma:=\{v\in\Vmax: L(\bar\sigma(v))>L(\sigma(v))\},\qquad
  S^\tau:=\{u\in\Vmin: U(\bar\tau(u))<U(\tau(u))\},
\]
and let each player veto the other's switches:
\[
  C_{\max}:=\{v\in S_\sigma: U(\bar\sigma(v))\ge U(\sigma(v))\},\qquad
  C_{\min}:=\{u\in S^\tau: L(\bar\tau(u))\le L(\tau(u))\}.
\]
The rule \emph{BSI} iterates
$(\sigma,\tau)\mapsto(\sigma[C_{\max}],\tau[C_{\min}])$ and halts when
$C_{\max}=C_{\min}=\emptyset$. The \emph{strict} variant replaces the two
veto conditions by $U(\bar\sigma(v))>U(\sigma(v))$ and
$L(\bar\tau(u))<L(\tau(u))$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[BSI never stalls]\label{thm:bsi-nostall}
Let $G$ be stopping and $(\sigma,\tau)$ any pair state. If
$C_{\max}=C_{\min}=\emptyset$ then $L=U=w^\ast$; in particular $\sigma$ and
$\tau$ are both optimal and $S_\sigma=S^\tau=\emptyset$. The same holds for
the strict variant.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
This is the argument of \Cref{lem:max-deficit} with $w^\ast$ replaced by
$U$. Put $g:=U-L\ge0$ (\Cref{cor:comparison}), $M:=\max g$ and
$Z:=\operatorname{argmax}g$, and suppose $M>0$. The sinks have $g=0$, so
$Z$ contains none. By \Cref{thm:contraction}, $L$ is the unique fixed point
of $T_\sigma$ and $U$ of $T^\tau$, so $L(x)=L(\sigma(x))$ and
$U(x)=\max_iU(x^{(i)})$ at $x\in\Vmax$, and dually at $x\in\Vmin$.

If $x\in Z$ is average then $g(x)=\tfrac12(g(x^{(0)})+g(x^{(1)}))$ with both
terms at most $M$, so both successors lie in $Z$.

Let $x\in Z\cap\Vmax$, $p:=\sigma(x)$, $q:=\bar\sigma(x)$. If
$x\notin S_\sigma$ then $L(q)\le L(p)$; choose $y\in\{p,q\}$ with
$U(y)=U(x)$; then $L(y)\le L(p)=L(x)$, so
$M=U(y)-L(x)\le U(y)-L(y)=g(y)\le M$ and $y\in Z$. If $x\in S_\sigma$ then
$C_{\max}=\emptyset$ forces $U(q)<U(p)$ --- for the strict variant,
$U(q)\le U(p)$ --- so $U(x)=U(p)$ and $M=g(p)$, putting $p\in Z$. The case
$x\in Z\cap\Vmin$ is dual, using $C_{\min}=\emptyset$.

So $Z$ is a nonempty sink-free set in which every average vertex keeps both
successors and every controlled vertex keeps one: by \Cref{lem:trapchar} it
is a trap, contradicting stopping. Hence $M=0$, that is $L=U$; and
$L\le w^\ast\le U$ gives $L=U=w^\ast$.
\end{proof}

The proof used the two vetoes only at the vertices of $Z$, and that is worth
isolating, because it turns \Cref{thm:bsi-nostall} from a halting statement
into a progress statement.

\begin{lemma}[Pair localisation]\label{lem:bsi-pairloc}
Let $G$ be stopping and $(\sigma,\tau)$ a pair state with
$M:=\max(U-L)>0$, and put $Z:=\operatorname{argmax}(U-L)$. Then
$Z\cap(C_{\max}\cup C_{\min})\ne\emptyset$, in the veto variant and in the
strict variant alike.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Suppose not. Inspect the proof of \Cref{thm:bsi-nostall}: the hypothesis
$C_{\max}=\emptyset$ is used there only at a vertex $x\in Z\cap\Vmax$ with
$x\in S_\sigma$, and $C_{\min}=\emptyset$ only at the dual vertices of
$Z\cap\Vmin$. Under $Z\cap(C_{\max}\cup C_{\min})=\emptyset$ each such $x$
lies in $Z$ and hence outside $C_{\max}$ (resp.\ $C_{\min}$), which is
exactly what those steps need. The four cases therefore go through
verbatim and make $Z$ a nonempty sink-free trap, contradicting
\Cref{lem:trapchar}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[A lexicographic potential, and the round count]\label{cor:bsi-levels}
Along any BSI run on a stopping game the pair
$\Phi(\sigma,\tau):=(M,|Z|)$ strictly decreases in the lexicographic order
at every productive round. Consequently the run halts, and its length is
at most $|V|$ times the number of distinct values $M$ takes along it.

The second sentence is a reparametrisation and not a bound: every level
lasts at least one round, so the number of levels lies between
$(\text{rounds})/|V|$ and $\text{rounds}$. What is new is the first
sentence --- a strictly decreasing progress measure for a rule for which
none was known --- and the fact that it is \emph{lexicographic}, which is
why \Cref{thm:impedance}, about scalar measures of bounded range, does
not apply to it; \Cref{cor:no-height} does not either, for the reason
\Cref{rem:bsi-br} gives: its measures live on Max strategies and must
increase along \emph{every} improving switch, whereas $\Phi$ is attached
to the pairs of one rule.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Write $\sigma':=\sigma[C_{\max}]$, $\tau':=\tau[C_{\min}]$, $L':=\val_{\sigma'}$,
$U':=\val^{\tau'}$, $g:=U-L$ and $g':=U'-L'$. Since $C_{\max}\subseteq S_\sigma$,
\Cref{lem:switch} gives $L'\ge L$; applying it in $\bar G$ and carrying the
result back by \Cref{lem:duality} gives $U'\le U$. So $g'\le g$ pointwise
and $M'\le M$.

By \Cref{lem:bsi-pairloc} there is $x\in Z\cap(C_{\max}\cup C_{\min})$; say
$x\in Z\cap C_{\max}$, the other case being dual. Then
$\sigma'(x)=\bar\sigma(x)$, so by \Cref{thm:lfp-general}(a) and $L'\ge L$
and $x\in S_\sigma$,
\[
  L'(x)=L'(\bar\sigma(x))\ \ge\ L(\bar\sigma(x))\ >\ L(\sigma(x))=L(x),
\]
whence $g'(x)<g(x)=M$. If $M'<M$ the first coordinate has dropped. If
$M'=M$ then $Z'=\{v:g'(v)=M\}\subseteq\{v:g(v)\ge M\}=Z$ while $x\in Z$
and $x\notin Z'$, so $|Z'|<|Z|$. Since $\Phi$ takes values in a finite
set, the run halts, and between two consecutive drops of $M$ the second
coordinate can fall at most $|V|$ times.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[A vertex can be switched twice]\label{prop:bsi-twice}
There is a stopping SSG on six vertices, with both players present, on
which BSI switches one vertex twice. Take $V'=\{0,1,2,3\}$ with
$\Vavg=\{0\}$, $\Vmax=\{1,3\}$, $\Vmin=\{2\}$ and
\[
  0\mapsto(t_1,2),\qquad 1\mapsto(t_0,t_1),\qquad
  2\mapsto(1,t_0),\qquad 3\mapsto(1,0),
\]
so that $w^\ast=(\tfrac12,1,0,1)$; start from
$\sigma=\{1\mapsto t_0,\ 3\mapsto1\}$ and $\tau=\{2\mapsto1\}$. The run is
\[
\begin{array}{c|cccc}
 \text{round} & L & U & (M,|Z|) & C_{\max}\ /\ C_{\min}\\\hline
 0 & (\tfrac12,0,0,0) & (1,1,1,1) & (1,3) & \{1,3\}\ /\ \{2\}\\
 1 & (\tfrac12,1,0,\tfrac12) & (\tfrac12,1,0,1) & (\tfrac12,1) & \{3\}\ /\ \emptyset\\
 2 & w^\ast & w^\ast & (0,6) & \emptyset\ /\ \emptyset
\end{array}
\]
and vertex $3$ is switched at both productive rounds, returning to its
original successor. Hence no argument of the form ``each controlled vertex
is switched at most once'' is available for BSI, and \Cref{cor:bsi-levels}
cannot be improved to a bound of $|C|$ rounds by such means.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The game is stopping: vertices $0$ and $1$ have a sink successor, so by
\Cref{lem:trapchar} neither lies in a trap; $2$ then has no successor in a
trap, and neither does $3$. The displayed $w^\ast$ is a fixed point of
$T$, hence the value by \Cref{cor:comparison}. The table is a direct
computation of $\val_\sigma$ as the minimum over all positional $\tau$ and
of $\val^\tau$ as the maximum over all positional $\sigma$, and of the two
veto conditions of \Cref{def:bsi}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The exact gap for BSI]\label{rem:bsi-gap}
\Cref{cor:bsi-levels} replaces the crude $2N4^{a}$ of
\Cref{cor:bsi-correct} by $|V|$ times the number of \emph{levels} --- the
distinct values of $M=\max(\val^\tau-\val_\sigma)$ along the run --- and
nothing here bounds that number --- indeed, as \Cref{cor:bsi-levels}
notes, the levels are within a factor $|V|$ of the rounds, so the
corollary names the quantity to be bounded rather than reducing it. What
it adds is a measure that provably falls. The question is sharp, and it is
a dichotomy:
\begin{quote}
either the number of distinct values of $M$ along a BSI run on a stopping
SSG is bounded by a polynomial in $N$, in which case
\Cref{cor:bsi-correct} and \Cref{thm:stopping-transform} put
\Cref{prob:main} in $\mathsf P$; or there is a family of stopping SSGs on
polynomially many vertices along whose BSI runs some controlled vertex is
switched superpolynomially often.
\end{quote}
The second horn is what \Cref{prop:bsi-twice} leaves open and what the
$2^{-\Theta(a)}$ grid of \Cref{lem:denominator-sharp} permits: $M$ lives
on that grid, so the levels could a priori number $4^{a}$. Two structural
facts constrain the search for such a family: \Cref{prop:bsi-oneplayer}
below, which rules out every one-player instance --- and therefore all of
$L_n$, $H_m$, $\mathrm{CC}(L,m)$ and $\mathrm{WD}(e,j,m)$ --- and the
observation that the disjoint union of a game with its dual does not
couple the two tracks, each running in its own component and neither
seeing the other's values. A family for BSI must be genuinely two-player
and must couple the tracks through shared vertices.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[BSI is trivial with one player]\label{prop:bsi-oneplayer}
Let $G$ be a stopping SSG with $\Vmin=\emptyset$. Then BSI halts within
$|\Vmax|$ productive rounds from every starting strategy, in both
variants.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
With $\Vmin$ empty there is one Min strategy, the empty one, and
$U=\val^\tau=w^\ast$. Call $v\in\Vmax$ \emph{greedy} if
$w^\ast(\sigma(v))=\max_iw^\ast(v^{(i)})$. The veto
$U(\bar\sigma(v))\ge U(\sigma(v))$ says exactly that $\bar\sigma(v)$
attains that maximum, so every switch performed makes its vertex greedy;
and a greedy vertex stays greedy, since a later switch of it again
requires the other successor to attain the maximum. So the set of
non-greedy vertices never grows. It strictly shrinks at every productive
round: if $\sigma\notin\Opt$ then \Cref{lem:max-deficit} supplies a
$v\in\Vmax$ that is wrong at $\sigma$ --- so
$w^\ast(\bar\sigma(v))>w^\ast(\sigma(v))$, and $v$ passes the veto in
both variants --- and strictly switchable, so $v\in C_{\max}$ and $v$ is
non-greedy before the round and greedy after. Hence there are at most
$|\Vmax|$ productive rounds, and by \Cref{thm:bsi-nostall} the halt is at
an optimal pair.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[BSI is a correct algorithm]\label{cor:bsi-correct}
On a stopping SSG, from any pair state, BSI terminates at a pair
$(\sigma,\tau)$ with $\val_\sigma=\val^\tau=w^\ast$, both strategies
optimal. Each round costs two linear programmes, and the number of rounds
is at most $2N4^{a}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By \Cref{thm:bsi-nostall} the halting condition holds only at an optimal
pair, so before that at least one of $C_{\max},C_{\min}$ is nonempty.
Switching a nonempty subset of $S_\sigma$ strictly increases $\val_\sigma$
and switching a nonempty subset of $S^\tau$ strictly decreases $\val^\tau$,
so \Cref{thm:switch-count} applied to $G$ and to $\bar G$ --- which is
stopping because exchanging the sinks does not change the traps --- bounds
each track by $N4^{a}$.
\end{proof}

Collapsing one of the two tracks gives a genuinely one-sided rule that
still never stalls, and it costs a weaker hypothesis than
\Cref{thm:bsi-nostall} needs: nothing at all is assumed about
$C_{\min}$.

\begin{proposition}[Best-response restart never stalls]\label{prop:bsi-br}
Let $G$ be stopping, let $\sigma$ be a Max strategy with
$S_\sigma\ne\emptyset$, and let $\tau$ be \emph{any} Min best response to
$\sigma$, that is any positional $\tau$ with
$\val_{\sigma,\tau}=\val_\sigma$. Then $C_{\max}\ne\emptyset$, for the
veto variant and for the strict variant alike. Consequently the rule
\[
  R_{\mathrm{BR}}(\sigma)\;:=\;\sigma\bigl[\{v\in S_\sigma:
    \val^\tau(\bar\sigma(v))\ge\val^\tau(\sigma(v))\}\bigr],
  \qquad\tau\ \text{a best response to }\sigma,
\]
is a switching rule in the sense of \Cref{def:rule}: it is computable in
polynomial time by \Cref{thm:eval-stopfree}, it switches only strictly
improving vertices, and it halts only at an optimal $\sigma$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Put $L:=\val_\sigma$, $U:=\val^\tau$, $g:=U-L$, $M:=\max g$ and
$Z:=\operatorname{argmax}g$. Since $S_\sigma\ne\emptyset$, $\sigma$ is not
optimal (\Cref{lem:local-global}), so $L\ne w^\ast$; with
$L\le w^\ast\le U$ this gives $M>0$. The sinks have $g=0$, so $Z$ contains
neither. Suppose $Z\cap C_{\max}=\emptyset$.

At an average $x\in Z$, $g(x)=\tfrac12(g(x^{(0)})+g(x^{(1)}))$ with both
terms at most $M$, so both successors lie in $Z$.

Let $x\in Z\cap\Vmin$ and $p:=\tau(x)$. Then $U(x)=U(p)$, because $\tau$ is
frozen in $\val^\tau$; and $L(x)=L(p)$, because
$\val_{\sigma,\tau}=\val_\sigma$ and $\val_{\sigma,\tau}(x)=\val_{\sigma,\tau}(\tau(x))$.
So $g(p)=g(x)=M$ and $p\in Z$ --- and this is the step at which the
hypothesis on $C_{\min}$ in \Cref{thm:bsi-nostall} is replaced by the
best-response property.

Let $x\in Z\cap\Vmax$, $p:=\sigma(x)$, $q:=\bar\sigma(x)$, so $L(x)=L(p)$
and $U(x)=\max(U(p),U(q))$. If $U(p)\ge U(q)$ then $U(x)=U(p)$ and
$g(p)=U(x)-L(x)=M$, so $p\in Z$. Otherwise $U(q)>U(p)$, whence
$U(x)=U(q)$; were $x\in S_\sigma$ we should have $x\in C_{\max}$ in either
variant, against $Z\cap C_{\max}=\emptyset$; so $x\notin S_\sigma$, that is
$L(q)\le L(p)=L(x)$, and therefore
$g(q)=U(x)-L(q)\ge U(x)-L(x)=M$, forcing $g(q)=M$ and $q\in Z$.

So $Z$ is a nonempty sink-free set in which every average vertex keeps both
successors and every controlled vertex keeps one; by \Cref{lem:trapchar} it
is a trap, contradicting stopping. Hence $Z\cap C_{\max}\ne\emptyset$.

For the consequence: $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$ switches a nonempty subset of
$S_\sigma$ whenever $S_\sigma\ne\emptyset$, so by \Cref{lem:switch} the
vector $\val_\sigma$ strictly increases at each round and no strategy
recurs; the rule can therefore halt only at $S_\sigma=\emptyset$, which is
optimality by \Cref{lem:local-global}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:bsi-br}
\Cref{prop:bsi-br} is not \Cref{thm:bsi-nostall} specialised: BSI with
$\tau$ reset to a best response after every round is a strictly one-sided
rule, its Min track carrying no state, and the proposition says that
Max's veto by $\val^\tau$ alone is already enough. Nor is it
Hoffman--Karp, which switches all of $S_\sigma$; $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$
switches the subset that Min's own value function endorses, and that
subset can be small enough to change the order of the round count. On the
ladder $L_n$ of \Cref{def:ladder}, exhaustively over all $2^{n}$ starting
strategies and in exact arithmetic, the worst case for $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$
is $\lfloor\log_2n\rfloor+1$ rounds --- $1,2,2,3,3,3,3,4$ at
$n=1,\dots,8$ --- where all-switches takes $n$. On the two-player
disjoint union of $L_n$ with its dual the same counts were seen, but over
a sample of starting pairs and not exhaustively, so that half is a
measurement of a sample and nothing more. On $G^{\sharp}$ of
\Cref{prop:auso-seven}, where all-switches attains its extremal $7$ rounds,
$R_{\mathrm{BR}}$ takes at most $5$ --- exhaustively over all sixteen
starts, in both veto variants; Min's best response is unique at every Max
strategy of $G^{\sharp}$, so the freedom \Cref{prop:bsi-br} allows does
not arise there --- and along the longest run, from
$(c_1,c_2,c_3,c_4)=(0,1,1,1)$, the switch sets are
$\{c_1,c_3\},\{c_4\},\{c_2,c_3\},\{c_3\},\{c_1\}$: the Max vertex $c_3$
is switched three times and $c_1$ twice. So $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$ is not
bounded by the number of Max vertices for the trivial reason, and
\Cref{prop:bsi-twice}'s phenomenon occurs on an instance the document
already carries. (An earlier version of this remark reported four rounds
and a single vertex switched twice; the count above was recomputed from
the printed normal form.)

Being one-sided, $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$ is a candidate for the barriers, and
they miss it for the same reason they miss \Cref{def:bsi}. It is not
residue-blind: on the normal form $N'(G)$ of \Cref{lem:normalform} no
controlled vertex has a controlled successor, and a residue-blind rule is
by definition all-switches there, whereas $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$ still filters
$S_\sigma$ by an order --- that of $\val^\tau$ --- which $\val_\sigma$ does
not determine. So neither \Cref{thm:normalform-barrier} nor
\Cref{thm:window-barrier} applies, and \Cref{cor:no-height} does not
either, its measures being required to increase along \emph{every}
improving switch. The round count of $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$ is open, exactly as
that of \Cref{def:bsi} is; \Cref{thm:readout} below gives a family on
which both take $\Theta(N)$ rounds where all-switches takes one, and
\Cref{thm:bsi-tracks} bounds the strict variant by $O(N2^{\min(m,k)})$.

Attribution, and it is owed twice over. Carrying both players'
strategies and letting each filter the other's switches is the symmetric
strategy improvement of Schewe--Trivedi--Varghese for parity games; and
the pair-carrying form with mutual vetoes that \Cref{def:bsi} writes down
is, we are advised, the generalised symmetric strategy iteration of
van~Dijk, Loho and Maat, of which $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$ is the variant with
one track collapsed to a best response. Neither \Cref{def:bsi} nor
\Cref{prop:bsi-br} should be read as new: what is offered here is the
transcription to Condon's model, proofs from this document's own lemmas,
and the quantitative statements
\Cref{lem:bsi-pairloc,cor:bsi-levels,prop:bsi-twice,prop:bsi-oneplayer}.
The identification with van~Dijk--Loho--Maat is recorded on the strength
of an audit and has not been checked against the source here; if it holds
then the round count of \Cref{def:bsi} is their open question and not
ours.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Self-dual games: BSI is a one-sided rule]\label{prop:selfdual-bsi}
Let $G$ be stopping and \emph{self-dual}: there is an involution $\phi$ of
$V$ with $\phi(t_0)=t_1$, $\phi(\Vmax)=\Vmin$, $\phi(\Vavg)=\Vavg$ and
$\phi(x^{(i)})=\phi(x)^{(i)}$ for every non-sink $x$, so that $\phi$ is an
isomorphism of $G$ onto its dual $\bar G$. For a Max strategy $\sigma$ let
$\phi(\sigma)$ be the Min strategy $\phi(v)\mapsto\phi(\sigma(v))$. Then
$\val^{\phi(\sigma)}=1-\val_\sigma\circ\phi$ on all of $V$, and from a
mirrored pair $(\sigma,\phi(\sigma))$ every round of \Cref{def:bsi}
produces a mirrored pair, with $C_{\min}=\phi(C_{\max})$ and
\[
  C_{\max}=\bigl\{v\in S_\sigma:\ \val_\sigma(\phi(\bar\sigma(v)))\le
  \val_\sigma(\phi(\sigma(v)))\bigr\}
\]
($<$ for the strict variant). So on self-dual games from mirrored starts
BSI is the one-sided rule $\mathrm{MR}\colon\sigma\mapsto\sigma[C_{\max}]$,
which reads $\val_\sigma$ and $\phi$ only, and by \Cref{thm:bsi-nostall}
it is a switching rule in the sense of \Cref{def:rule}: it endorses the
switch of $v$ to $q$ exactly when Min's best response to $\sigma$ at the
mirror vertex $\phi(v)$ weakly (strictly) prefers $\phi(q)$. Self-dual
games are thereby reduced to a one-sided rule, not excluded:
$\mathrm{MR}$ is covered by no barrier of this document, for the reason
\Cref{rem:bsi-br} gives for $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Freezing Min to $\phi(\sigma)$ in $G$ leaves a Max-only stopping game
whose dual $\phi$ carries isomorphically onto the game with Max frozen to
$\sigma$; \Cref{lem:duality} gives $\val^{\phi(\sigma)}=1-\val_\sigma\circ\phi$
off the sinks, and at the sinks both sides agree because $\phi$ exchanges
them. Substituting $U=1-L\circ\phi$, $L=\val_\sigma$, in \Cref{def:bsi}:
$\phi(v)\in S^{\phi(\sigma)}$ iff $L(\bar\sigma(v))>L(\sigma(v))$ iff
$v\in S_\sigma$, and the Min veto at $\phi(v)$ reads
$L(\phi\bar\sigma(v))\le L(\phi\sigma(v))$, which is the Max veto
$U(\bar\sigma(v))\ge U(\sigma(v))$ at $v$. Hence $C_{\min}=\phi(C_{\max})$
and $\phi(\sigma)[C_{\min}]=\phi(\sigma[C_{\max}])$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Both tracks must be long]\label{thm:bsi-tracks}
Let a run of \Cref{def:bsi}, in either variant, on a stopping $G$ with
$|\Vmax|=m$ and $|\Vmin|=k$ have $R$ productive rounds, $A$ of them with
$C_{\max}\ne\emptyset$ and $B$ with $C_{\min}\ne\emptyset$. Then
\[
  R\le m+(m+1)B,\qquad R\le k+(k+1)A,\qquad B\le2^{k}-1,\qquad A\le2^{m}-1,
\]
so $R\le\min\{m+(m+1)(2^{k}-1),\,k+(k+1)(2^{m}-1)\}=O(N2^{\min(m,k)})$, and
a superpolynomial run forces $B\ge(R-m)/(m+1)$ and $A\ge(R-k)/(k+1)$:
both tracks must be superpolynomially long. (The class
$\min(m,k)=O(\log N)$ is trivially in P --- enumerate the smaller player's
polynomially many positional strategies and apply \Cref{thm:one-player}
to each --- so the bound is a statement about the rule, not a new
polynomial class.)
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Call $v\in\Vmax$ \emph{$U$-greedy} at $\sigma$ if $U(\sigma(v))\ge U(\bar\sigma(v))$
and let $\Phi(\sigma,U)\in[0,m]$ count them. A round with
$C_{\min}=\emptyset$ leaves $\tau$, hence $U$, unchanged; a switched $v$
with $U(\bar\sigma(v))>U(\sigma(v))$ was not $U$-greedy and becomes so,
one with equality is $U$-greedy before and after, and the unswitched
vertices keep their status: $\Phi$ does not decrease, and increases by at
least one if some switched vertex is strictly preferred by $U$. In the
strict variant every switched vertex is. In the veto variant, at a
productive round $M=\max(U-L)>0$ (else $L=U=w^{\ast}$ and nothing is
switchable), and \Cref{lem:bsi-pairloc} applied in the strict reading at
the same pair state gives $Z\cap(C^{>}_{\max}\cup C^{<}_{\min})\ne\emptyset$
with $C^{<}_{\min}\subseteq C_{\min}=\emptyset$, so some strictly preferred
vertex is switched. Hence a maximal block of consecutive rounds with
$C_{\min}=\emptyset$ has at most $m$ rounds; there are at most $B+1$
blocks, so $R\le m(B+1)+B$. Dually $R\le k(A+1)+A$. Every round with
$C_{\min}\ne\emptyset$ switches Min vertices that Min's own value
$\val^{\tau}$ endorses, so $\val^{\tau}$ decreases and strictly somewhere
(\Cref{lem:switch} in $\bar G$), and the Min strategies of those rounds
are pairwise distinct: $B\le2^{k}-1$, and dually $A\le2^{m}-1$.
\end{proof}

This is the first bound for \Cref{def:bsi} that does not go through the
value grid --- \Cref{cor:bsi-correct} gives $2N4^{a}$, and $a$ can be
$\Theta(N)$ while $\min(m,k)$ is $O(1)$ --- and its content is that a
superpolynomial family cannot be built by making one player work while
the other idles, the failure mode of the disjoint union of
\Cref{rem:bsi-gap}; it is \Cref{prop:bsi-oneplayer} relativised to a
frozen guide. Two families from the round-$15$ best-response route now
calibrate the rules.

\begin{definition}[The readout cascade $\mathrm{RD}(n)$]\label{def:readout-cascade}
Let $f(x):=x/2+1/4$, $\theta_j:=\tfrac12-4^{-j}$ and $B_j:=\tfrac12+4^{-j}$,
so that $f(\theta_j)<\theta_{j+1}<B_{j+1}<f(B_j)$. $\mathrm{RD}(n)$ has
$H=\mathrm{avg}(t_0,t_1)$, dyadic gadgets $\Theta_j$ and $b_j$ of values
$\theta_j$ and $B_j$ (\Cref{lem:dyadic-row}: $\Theta_j=\mathrm{avg}(e_{2j-1},t_0)$
over a shared chain of values $1-2^{-i}$ and $b_j=\mathrm{avg}(t_1,d_{2j-1})$
over a shared chain of values $2^{-i}$, $O(1)$ vertices each), and for $1\le j\le n$
\[
  a_1:=t_0,\quad a_j:=\mathrm{avg}(u_{j-1},H)\ (j\ge2),\quad
  v_j\in\Vmax,\ v_j\to(a_j,b_j),\quad u_j\in\Vmin,\ u_j\to(v_j,\Theta_j),
\]
with $\sigma_0(v_j)=a_j$ and $\tau_0(u_j)=v_j$. It is acyclic, hence
stopping, with $N=9n-2$ vertices and $|\Vmax|=|\Vmin|=n$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[All-switches one round, best-response restart $n$, BSI $2n$]\label{thm:readout}
On $\mathrm{RD}(n)$: all-switches halts after at most one round from
every Max strategy (zero at the optimum); from $\sigma_0$ the rule $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$ of
\Cref{prop:bsi-br} takes exactly $n$ rounds, in both variants and for
every choice of best response, switching $v_{t+1}$ alone at round $t$;
and \Cref{def:bsi} from $(\sigma_0,\tau_0)$ takes exactly $2n$ rounds,
alternating $C_{\max}=\{v_{t+1}\}$ with $C_{\min}=\{u_{t+1}\}$. Every
controlled vertex is switched once and every run halts at $w^{\ast}$. So
the round count of either rule exceeds that of all-switches by a factor
$\Theta(N)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $\sigma_t$ have $\sigma_t(v_j)=b_j$ for $j\le t$ and $a_j$ for
$j>t$, and $L_t:=\val_{\sigma_t}$, so $L_t(u_j)=\min(L_t(v_j),\theta_j)$.
By induction on $j$, $L_t(v_j)=B_j$ and $L_t(u_j)=\theta_j$ for $j\le t$,
and $L_t(v_j)<\theta_j$ for $j>t$: indeed
$L_t(v_{t+1})=f(L_t(u_t))=f(\theta_t)<\theta_{t+1}$ (with $L_0(v_1)=0$),
and for $j>t+1$, $L_t(v_j)=f(L_t(u_{j-1}))<f(\theta_{j-1})<\theta_j$.
Hence $S_{\sigma_t}=\{v_j:j>t\}$; for an arbitrary $\sigma$ the same
computation gives $L(a_j)\le f(\theta_{j-1})<B_j$, so
$S_\sigma=\{v_j:\sigma(v_j)=a_j\}$ and one all-switches round lands at
$\sigma_n$, which is optimal. The best response to $\sigma_t$ is unique:
$\tau_t(u_j)=\Theta_j$ for $j\le t$ and $v_j$ for $j>t$ --- Min
\emph{reads off} which Max vertices have switched. With
$U_t:=\val^{\tau_t}$ one has $U_t(a_{t+1})=f(\theta_t)<B_{t+1}$, so
$v_{t+1}$ passes both vetoes, while $U_t(a_{t+2})=f(B_{t+1})>B_{t+2}$
and inductively $U_t(a_j)>B_j$ for all $j>t+1$, so every other
switchable vertex is vetoed: $R_{\mathrm{BR}}(\sigma_t)=\sigma_{t+1}$.
For BSI the pair after $2t$ rounds is $(\sigma_t,\tau_t)$, where
$C_{\max}=\{v_{t+1}\}$ by the computation just made and
$C_{\min}=\emptyset$ because the veto of $u_j\in S^{\tau_t}$, $j>t$,
reads $\theta_j\le L_t(v_j)$, which fails; after that round
$L_{t+1}(v_{t+1})=B_{t+1}>\theta_{t+1}$ frees $u_{t+1}$ while the
unchanged $U_t$ still vetoes every $v_j$, $j>t+1$, so the next round is
$C_{\max}=\emptyset$, $C_{\min}=\{u_{t+1}\}$. (Exact arithmetic for
$n\le20$, all $2^{n}$ Max starts for $n\le3$, all best responses for
$n\le4$, by the route.)
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[One vertex switched $K$ times, with one player]\label{prop:leapfrog}
Fix $K\ge2$, $\eta:=2^{-(K+2)}$, $\gamma_j:=(-1)^{j+1}2^{j+1}\eta$ for
$1\le j<K$, $\gamma_K:=0$, $\Delta:=-\eta-\sum_j\gamma_j(1-2^{-j})$,
$\alpha_j:=\max(\gamma_j,0)$, $\beta_j:=\max(-\gamma_j,0)$,
$S:=\sum_j(\alpha_j+\beta_j)$, $a_p:=\tfrac12(1-S+\Delta)$ and
$a_q:=\tfrac12(1-S-\Delta)$, all nonnegative dyadic. Let $\mathrm{SD}(K)$
have the chain $k_j=\mathrm{avg}(t_1,k_{j-1})$ from $k_0:=t_0$, Max
vertices $x_j\to(x_{j-1},k_j)$ with $x_0:=t_1$ for $1\le j\le K$, two
average mixtures $p$, $q$ realised by \Cref{lem:dyadic-row}, $p$ putting
$\alpha_j$ on $x_j$, $a_p$ on $t_1$ and the rest on $t_0$, $q$ putting
$\beta_j$ on $x_j$, $a_q$ on $t_1$ and the rest on $t_0$, and the Max
vertex $v\to(p,q)$; start at $\sigma_0(x_j)=k_j$, $\sigma_0(v)=p$. This is
a one-player stopping SSG on $O(K^{2})$ vertices ($20,31,41,56,66$ for
$K=2,\dots,6$ in the build verified here, $18,23,31,39,49$ with the
canonical leaf order of \Cref{lem:dyadic-row}) with $|\Vmax|=K+1$ on
which all-switches takes exactly $K$ rounds, switching $\{x_{t+1},v\}$ at
round $t$: the vertex $v$ is switched $K$ times, at a cost of $\Theta(K)$
vertices per reversal, against the two reversals per vertex of
\Cref{prop:allswitch-overshoot}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
With $L_t$ the value at the strategy in which $x_1,\dots,x_t$ point at
their $x$-successor, $L_t(x_j)=1$ for $j\le t$ and $1-2^{-j}$ for $j>t$,
so $x_{t+1}$ is the only switchable chain vertex; and
$L_t(p)-L_t(q)=\Delta+\sum_{j\le t}\gamma_j+\sum_{j>t}\gamma_j(1-2^{-j})$,
whose successive differences are $\gamma_t2^{-t}=(-1)^{t+1}2\eta$, so
with the stated $\Delta$ it equals $(-1)^{t+1}\eta$ for $t<K$ and
$(-1)^{K}\eta$ at $t=K$: $v$, pointing at $p$ for even $t$ and at $q$ for
odd $t$, is switchable at every round $t<K$ and not at $t=K$. Stopping is
\Cref{lem:dyadic-row}. (Exact arithmetic for $K\le6$ here, and for the
route's two-player form below for $K\le20$.)
\end{proof}

The route presented this as a two-player phenomenon: adjoin the mirror
image --- a chain $h_j=\mathrm{avg}(t_0,h_{j-1})$ from $h_0:=t_1$, Min
vertices $u_j\to(u_{j-1},h_j)$ with $u_0:=t_0$, the mass that $p$ and $q$
put on $t_0$ redistributed onto the $u_j$ as $\beta_j$ and $\alpha_j$, and
a Min vertex $v'\to(q,p)$ --- and the game is self-dual under
$x_j\leftrightarrow u_j$, $p\leftrightarrow q$, $v\leftrightarrow v'$,
$t_0\leftrightarrow t_1$, so by \Cref{prop:selfdual-bsi} both variants of
\Cref{def:bsi} from the mirrored start make the same $K$ rounds with
$C_{\max}=\{x_{t+1},v\}$ and $C_{\min}=\{u_{t+1},v'\}$ (verified here for
$K\le4$). But $L(u_j)=0$ for every profile, and $v$ has in-degree zero, so
the Min player contributes nothing to the reversals: the apparatus only
makes the veto vacuous at $v$, where the guide agrees with the value. In
the notation of \Cref{thm:bsi-tracks} the two-player form has $A=B=K$
with $m=k=K+1$, far below the bound $m+(m+1)B$; it is the one-player
$\mathrm{SD}(K)$, with $B=0$ and $R=K=m-1$, that is within one of it.

\begin{remark}[What the two families settle]\label{rem:readout}
(i) The veto of \Cref{def:bsi} is not an accelerator: on $\mathrm{RD}(n)$
it costs a factor $\Theta(N)$ against all-switches, so the guide
$\val^{\tau}$ can be as adversarial as a superpolynomial family would need
it to be, and the measurements of \Cref{rem:bsi-br} were made on
instances ($L_n$, $G^{\sharp}$) whose all-switches runs are long relative
to $|\Vmax|$, where a veto has room to help, whereas on $\mathrm{RD}(n)$
the run is one round and the veto can only delay; the
round count of $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$ also depends on which best response is
chosen when it is not unique, by one round on a $10$-vertex instance of
the route. The family is at the natural ceiling of a rule that switches
one vertex per round (\Cref{thm:short-path}) and is no progress on any
lower bound; its content is the removal of the veto's credit.
(ii) Reversals are not expensive: $\mathrm{SD}(K)$ makes the number of
switches at one vertex unbounded at $\Theta(K)$ vertices per reversal,
$O(K^{2})$ in all. Each reversal
consumes a fresh driver $x_j$ that switches once, so a lemma of the form
``each reversal at $v$ spends a vertex'' is not refuted, but such a lemma
bounds the switches at $v$ only by the total number of switches, which is
what a superpolynomial family has to make superpolynomial anyway; the
obstruction is not the leapfrog but the recycling of its drivers.
Neither family exceeds $|C|$ rounds, and combining the readout with the
leapfrog is the obvious next construction. The attribution of
\Cref{rem:bsi-br} stands, with one addition: an auditor recalls, from
memory and unchecked against the source, that van Dijk, Loho and Maat
prove an exponential lower bound for symmetric strategy improvement on
parity games. If that is right, the round count of the rule is settled in
print for parity games and what is open here is only its behaviour on
stopping SSGs, which \Cref{thm:readout} begins to calibrate; we do not
know of a published family separating either rule from all-switches.
\end{remark}
\begin{lemma}[Same-successor bias]\label{lem:same-successor}
Let $v\in\Vmax$ have successors $p\in\Vmax$ and $q\in\Vavg$ with the same
successor pair $\{a,b\}$, as every $v_i$, $1\le i\le n-1$, of the ladder
$L_n$ of \Cref{def:ladder} has. Then $\val^{\tau}(p)\ge\val^{\tau}(q)$ for every
Min strategy $\tau$, with equality iff $\val^{\tau}(a)=\val^{\tau}(b)$; so
the strict variant of \Cref{def:bsi} never endorses the switch of $v$ from
$p$ to $q$, the veto variant endorses it only at a tie, and the switch from
$q$ to $p$, when improving, is endorsed by the veto variant always and by
the strict variant unless $\val^{\tau}(a)=\val^{\tau}(b)$. Moreover $p\to q$ is
$\val_\sigma$-improving at $v$ iff $p$ itself is strictly switchable at
$\sigma$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
$\val^{\tau}$ is the value of the game with Min frozen, so
$\val^{\tau}(p)=\max\{\val^{\tau}(a),\val^{\tau}(b)\}$ and
$\val^{\tau}(q)$ is the mean; a maximum dominates a mean, with equality
only when the arguments agree. For the last claim,
$\val_\sigma(p)=\val_\sigma(\sigma(p))$ and $\val_\sigma(q)$ is the mean
of $\val_\sigma(a),\val_\sigma(b)$ with $\sigma(p)\in\{a,b\}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Normalisation preserves BSI]\label{prop:bsi-normal}
Let $N'(G)$ be the bipartite normal form of \Cref{lem:normalform} with its
bijections $\sigma\mapsto\tilde\sigma$, $\tau\mapsto\tilde\tau$. Then
$\val_{\tilde\sigma}=\val_\sigma$ and $\val^{\tilde\tau}=\val^{\tau}$ on
$V(G)$, the two gadget vertices replacing an edge $(v,u)$ taking the value
of $u$; hence $S$, $S^{\tau}$, $C_{\max}$ and $C_{\min}$ coincide in both
variants, and the BSI runs on $(G,\sigma_0,\tau_0)$ and on
$(N'(G),\tilde\sigma_0,\tilde\tau_0)$ have the same length and pass through
corresponding pairs. The same holds for the $k$-blinding normalisation
$N_k$ of \Cref{def:kblind}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\Cref{lem:normalform}(iii) is the statement for $\val_\sigma$. The gadget
uses only average vertices and is inserted at exactly the edges whose
endpoints are both controlled, a condition invariant under exchanging the
players and the sinks, so $N'(\bar G)=\overline{N'(G)}$, and
\Cref{lem:normalform}(iii) applied to $\bar G$, carried back by
\Cref{lem:duality}, is the statement for $\val^{\tau}$; each test of
\Cref{def:bsi} compares two numbers that are unchanged. For $N_k$ use
\Cref{lem:kblind}(iii) on the Max side, and on the Min side the argument
just given for $N'$: $N_k$ subdivides every edge with average vertices, a
construction invariant under exchanging players and sinks, so
\Cref{lem:duality} carries the identity to $\val^{\tau}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[A Min vertex switched four times]\label{prop:q16}
Let $Q_{16}$ be the stopping game with eight Max vertices $c_1,\dots,c_8$
and eight Min vertices $c_9,\dots,c_{16}$ whose actions are the following
rows, in $64$-ths over $c_1,\dots,c_{16},t_1$, the remaining mass on $t_0$
(every row leaks, so \Cref{lem:dyadic-row} realises the game; with the
targets of each row laid on the leaves in vertex-index order it has $288$
vertices, a count its auditor reproduced):
\[
\begin{array}{r@{\ }l@{\qquad}l}
  c_{1}\ (\max): & \{c_{1}\!:\!1,\ c_{5}\!:\!1,\ c_{10}\!:\!1\} & \{c_{7}\!:\!1,\ c_{16}\!:\!1\}\\
  c_{2}\ (\max): & \{c_{4}\!:\!19,\ c_{8}\!:\!2\} & \{c_{2}\!:\!28,\ c_{5}\!:\!1,\ c_{12}\!:\!1\}\\
  c_{3}\ (\max): & \{c_{14}\!:\!1,\ t_1\!:\!1\} & \{c_{3}\!:\!2,\ c_{7}\!:\!26,\ c_{9}\!:\!1,\ c_{15}\!:\!1\}\\
  c_{4}\ (\max): & \{c_{2}\!:\!40,\ c_{4}\!:\!1,\ c_{9}\!:\!3\} & \{c_{5}\!:\!1,\ c_{8}\!:\!1,\ c_{16}\!:\!7\}\\
  c_{5}\ (\max): & \{c_{2}\!:\!16,\ c_{9}\!:\!1,\ c_{12}\!:\!1\} & \{c_{3}\!:\!56,\ c_{4}\!:\!2,\ c_{12}\!:\!1\}\\
  c_{6}\ (\max): & \{c_{14}\!:\!19\} & \{c_{3}\!:\!17,\ c_{9}\!:\!17\}\\
  c_{7}\ (\max): & \{c_{3}\!:\!19,\ c_{6}\!:\!18,\ c_{14}\!:\!2,\ c_{16}\!:\!1\} & \{c_{4}\!:\!3,\ c_{13}\!:\!48\}\\
  c_{8}\ (\max): & \{c_{1}\!:\!1,\ c_{11}\!:\!1,\ c_{12}\!:\!1\} & \{c_{5}\!:\!1,\ c_{11}\!:\!12,\ c_{14}\!:\!1,\ c_{16}\!:\!1\}\\
  c_{9}\ (\min): & \{c_{9}\!:\!1,\ t_1\!:\!35\} & \{c_{1}\!:\!2,\ c_{15}\!:\!1\}\\
  c_{10}\ (\min): & \{c_{9}\!:\!1,\ c_{11}\!:\!60\} & \{c_{5}\!:\!1,\ c_{6}\!:\!1,\ c_{9}\!:\!2,\ c_{11}\!:\!5,\ c_{15}\!:\!1,\ c_{16}\!:\!8\}\\
  c_{11}\ (\min): & \{c_{3}\!:\!1,\ c_{8}\!:\!26\} & \{c_{9}\!:\!10,\ c_{13}\!:\!47\}\\
  c_{12}\ (\min): & \{c_{1}\!:\!32,\ c_{10}\!:\!1\} & \{c_{5}\!:\!1\}\\
  c_{13}\ (\min): & \{c_{5}\!:\!1,\ c_{9}\!:\!1,\ c_{14}\!:\!15,\ c_{15}\!:\!24\} & \{c_{5}\!:\!5,\ c_{6}\!:\!16,\ c_{16}\!:\!2\}\\
  c_{14}\ (\min): & \{c_{7}\!:\!1,\ c_{9}\!:\!9,\ c_{13}\!:\!1\} & \{c_{2}\!:\!1,\ c_{9}\!:\!1,\ c_{15}\!:\!26\}\\
  c_{15}\ (\min): & \{c_{9}\!:\!16,\ c_{11}\!:\!6\} & \{c_{7}\!:\!43,\ c_{15}\!:\!1,\ t_1\!:\!13\}\\
  c_{16}\ (\min): & \{c_{8}\!:\!4,\ c_{13}\!:\!11,\ c_{15}\!:\!1\} & \{c_{5}\!:\!4,\ c_{8}\!:\!15,\ c_{11}\!:\!4,\ c_{13}\!:\!1\}
\end{array}
\]
From $\sigma_0=(0,0,1,1,0,0,1,0)$ and $\tau_0=(0,0,1,1,1,1,0,1)$ the veto
variant of \Cref{def:bsi} takes five rounds, with
$(C_{\max},C_{\min})=(\emptyset,\{9,10,11,16\})$, $(\{3\},\{10,12,13\})$,
$(\{5,6,7\},\{10,11\})$, $(\{2,8\},\{10\})$, $(\{4\},\emptyset)$: the Min
vertex $c_{10}$ is switched four times, $c_{11}$ twice, every other vertex
at most once, and the run halts
at $\val_\sigma=\val^{\tau}=w^{\ast}$. The strict variant from the same
start also takes five rounds and switches every vertex at most once: three
of the four reversals of $c_{10}$ are ties of $\val_\sigma$ at $c_{10}$
that the veto variant accepts and the strict one refuses. (Reproduced here
from the printed rows, $\val_\sigma$ and $\val^{\tau}$ as componentwise
extrema over all $2^{8}$ counter-strategies.)
\end{proposition}

This is the two-player counterpart of \Cref{prop:leapfrog}: reversals at
one vertex are cheap under the veto variant because a tie of the guide is
accepted, and \Cref{thm:bsi-tracks} bounds only the strictly endorsed
switches within a block.


\begin{lemma}[$R_{\mathrm{BR}}$ moves towards the double best response]\label{lem:double-br}
Let $G$ be stopping, $\tau$ a Min strategy and $U:=\val^{\tau}$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] $\sigma'\in\mathrm{BR}(\tau)$ if and only if $\sigma'$ is
  \emph{$U$-greedy}: $U(\sigma'(v))=\max_iU(v^{(i)})$ for every $v\in\Vmax$.
\item[(b)] For every Max strategy $\sigma$ and every $v\in\Vmax$,
\[
  U(\bar\sigma(v))>U(\sigma(v))\iff \sigma'(v)\ne\sigma(v)\ \text{for all }
  \sigma'\in\mathrm{BR}(\tau),\qquad
  U(\bar\sigma(v))\ge U(\sigma(v))\iff \sigma'(v)=\bar\sigma(v)\ \text{for some }
  \sigma'\in\mathrm{BR}(\tau).
\]
\end{enumerate}
Consequently, if $\tau$ is a Min best response to $\sigma$, the two variants of
\Cref{prop:bsi-br} switch
\[
  C^{>}_{\max}=S_\sigma\cap\!\!\bigcap_{\sigma'\in\mathrm{BR}(\tau)}\!\!\mathrm{Dis}(\sigma,\sigma'),
  \qquad
  C^{\ge}_{\max}=S_\sigma\cap\!\!\bigcup_{\sigma'\in\mathrm{BR}(\tau)}\!\!\mathrm{Dis}(\sigma,\sigma'),
\]
and when the Max best response $\sigma^{\ast}$ to $\tau$ is unique both are
$S_\sigma\cap\mathrm{Dis}(\sigma,\sigma^{\ast})$: one round of $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$
switches exactly those improving Max vertices at which $\sigma$ disagrees with
a best response to a best response to $\sigma$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) $U=\val^{\tau}$ is the unique fixed point of $T^{\tau}$
(\Cref{thm:contraction}, $G$ stopping), so $U(v)=\max_iU(v^{(i)})$ at
$v\in\Vmax$, $U(v)=U(\tau(v))$ at $v\in\Vmin$ and $U$ is the mean of its
successors at average vertices.  If $\sigma'$ is $U$-greedy then $U$ satisfies
all the equations of the profile $(\sigma',\tau)$, and by uniqueness of that
fixed point $U=\val_{\sigma',\tau}$.  Conversely if $\val_{\sigma',\tau}=U$
then $U(v)=U(\sigma'(v))$ at every $v\in\Vmax$, so $\sigma'(v)$ attains
$\max_iU(v^{(i)})$.  (b) is immediate from
$U(v)=\max\{U(v^{(0)}),U(v^{(1)})\}$ and (a).
\end{proof}





\begin{lemma}[Min's preference at a clamp reverses once]\label{lem:clamp-reverses-once}
Let $\sigma_0,\sigma_1,\dots$ be a run of a switching rule and let
$u\in\Vmin$ have successors $p,q$ with $\val_\sigma(q)=c$ constant.  A Min
best response $\tau_t$ to $\sigma_t$ has $\tau_t(u)=p$ when
$\val_{\sigma_t}(p)<c$ and $\tau_t(u)=q$ when $\val_{\sigma_t}(p)>c$.  Hence
if ties are broken by a fixed preference at $u$, the action $\tau_t(u)$ is a
monotone step function of $t$ and changes at most once along the run.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
$\val_{\sigma,\tau}=\val_\sigma$ forces $\tau(u)$ to attain
$\min_i\val_\sigma(u^{(i)})$ (dual of \Cref{lem:double-br}(a)), and
$t\mapsto\val_{\sigma_t}(p)-c$ is nondecreasing by \Cref{lem:switch}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Blocks, and the reordering bound]\label{thm:reorder}
Consider a run of $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$, in either variant, on a stopping $G$ with
$|\Vmax|=m$, producing $\sigma_0,\dots,\sigma_R$ with guides
$U_t:=\val^{\tau_t}$.  Put
\[
  d_t:=\bigl|\{v\in\Vmax:\ U_{t-1}(\sigma_t(v))=\textstyle\max_iU_{t-1}(v^{(i)})
   \ \text{and}\ U_t(\sigma_t(v))<\max_iU_t(v^{(i)})\}\bigr|\qquad(1\le t\le R-1),
\]
the number of Max vertices at which the guide's preference \emph{reverses away
from the current action} at round $t$.  Then
\[
  R\ \le\ m+\sum_{t=1}^{R-1}d_t .
\]
In particular $d_t=0$ whenever $U_t=U_{t-1}$, so a maximal block of
consecutive rounds sharing one guide has at most $m$ rounds and
$R\le m\bigl(1+\#\{t:\tau_t\ne\tau_{t-1}\}\bigr)$.  For \Cref{def:bsi}, in
either variant, the same argument gives
\[
  R\ \le\ m+B+\sum_{t}d_t\qquad\text{and dually}\qquad R\ \le\ k+A+\sum_{t}d'_t,
\]
with $A,B$ as in \Cref{thm:bsi-tracks}; since $d_t\le m$ and $d_t=0$ at a
round with $C_{\min}=\emptyset$, this refines \Cref{thm:bsi-tracks}'s
$R\le m+(m+1)B$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put $\Phi(\sigma,U):=|\{v\in\Vmax: U(\sigma(v))=\max_iU(v^{(i)})\}|\in[0,m]$.
Every $v\in C_{\max}$ has $U_t(\bar\sigma_t(v))\ge U_t(\sigma_t(v))$, so
$\bar\sigma_t(v)$ is $U_t$-greedy and $\sigma_{t+1}(v)$ is $U_t$-greedy;
unswitched vertices keep their status.  Hence
$\Phi(\sigma_{t+1},U_t)=\Phi(\sigma_t,U_t)+a_t$, where $a_t:=|C^{>}_{\max}|$
counts the switched vertices that were \emph{not} $U_t$-greedy.  Next,
$\Phi(\sigma_{t+1},U_{t+1})\ge\Phi(\sigma_{t+1},U_t)-d_{t+1}$ by the
definition of $d_{t+1}$.  Summing over $t$ and using $0\le\Phi\le m$,
\[
  \sum_{t=0}^{R-1}a_t\ \le\ m+\sum_{t=1}^{R-1}d_t .
\]
For $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$, \Cref{prop:bsi-br} in its strict reading gives
$C^{>}_{\max}\ne\emptyset$ at every round with $S_\sigma\ne\emptyset$, so
$a_t\ge1$ and $R\le\sum a_t$.  For \Cref{def:bsi}, $a_t\ge1$ can fail; but by
\Cref{lem:bsi-pairloc} in its strict reading every productive round has
$C^{>}_{\max}\ne\emptyset$ or $C^{<}_{\min}\ne\emptyset$, and the rounds of
the second kind have $C_{\min}\ne\emptyset$ and so number at most $B$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Clamped games are easy for $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$]\label{cor:clamp}
Let $G$ be stopping with $|\Vmax|=m$, $|\Vmin|=k$, and suppose every Min
vertex has a constant successor.  Then $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$, run with any fixed
tie-breaking preference at each Min vertex, halts within $m(k+1)$ rounds from
every start.  Dually, if every Max vertex has a constant successor, every
switching rule halts within $2m$ rounds (a Max vertex over a variable and a constant switches at most twice along any improving run, \Cref{lem:switch}).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By \Cref{lem:clamp-reverses-once} each Min vertex changes its chosen action at most once,
so $\tau_t$ changes at most $k$ times and the run splits into at most $k+1$
blocks; each has at most $m$ rounds by \Cref{thm:reorder}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:clamp}
\Cref{cor:clamp} is why every family this document carries is fast for
$R_{\mathrm{BR}}$: $\mathrm{RD}(n)$ has all its $\Theta_j$ constant
($m=k=n$, bound $n(n+1)$, truth $n$), $L_n$, $H_m$, $\mathrm{CC}(L,m)$,
$\mathrm{WD}(e,j,m)$ and $\mathrm{SD}(K)$ have $k=0$, and
\Cref{prop:bsi-oneplayer} is the case $k=0$ of the corollary.  Together with
the two-switch bound for a constant option (\Cref{lem:switch}, and the remark after \Cref{lem:max-tree}) it says what a superpolynomial family must contain:
\emph{both} a Max vertex whose two options are non-constant and a Min vertex
whose two options are non-constant, the Min one reversing superpolynomially
often.  This subsumes the round-$15$ auditor's refutation of the $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$
route's proposed counter (a Max vertex over a constant gadget) and gives its
Min-side twin.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[$W_2$: three rounds with two Max vertices]\label{prop:w2}
Let $W_2$ be the stopping SSG whose harmonic normal form
(\Cref{lem:dyadic-row}) has $\Vmax=\{v_0,v_1\}$, $\Vmin=\{u\}$ and rows
\[
\begin{array}{r@{\ }l@{\qquad}l}
 v_0\ (\max): & \tfrac{13}{16}\,v_1 & \tfrac1{32}v_1+\tfrac{23}{32}u+\tfrac5{32}t_1\\
 v_1\ (\max): & \tfrac{29}{32}\,u   & \tfrac14\,t_1\\
 u\ (\min):   & \tfrac{15}{16}\,v_0 & \tfrac1{32}\,t_1
\end{array}
\]
(the missing mass leaks to $t_0$; every row is dyadic of denominator $32$, so
\Cref{lem:dyadic-row} realises $W_2$ as a stopping SSG on $34$ vertices: $2$
Max, $1$ Min, $29$ average and the two sinks).  Then
$w^{\ast}=(\tfrac{13}{64},\tfrac14,\tfrac1{32})$ and:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] from $\sigma_0=(0,0)$, $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$ takes exactly three
 productive rounds, $(0,0)\to(1,0)\to(1,1)\to(0,1)$, switching $v_0$, then
 $v_1$, then $v_0$ again; the run is the same in the veto and the strict
 variant and for \emph{every} choice of best response, and no comparison made
 along it is a tie;
\item[(ii)] all-switches takes at most two rounds from every start, and
 $h^{\ast}(2)=2$ (\Cref{prop:auso-census}), so $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$ beats the
 all-switches ceiling already at $m=2$;
\item[(iii)] $R=3=2^{m}-1$ is the absolute maximum for $m=2$, so
 $h_{\mathrm{BR}}(2)=3$ against $h^{\ast}(2)=2$, $h^{\ast}_{HK}(2)=2$;
\item[(iv)] \Cref{def:bsi} from $(\sigma_0,\tau_0)=((0,0),0)$ takes exactly
 four rounds in both variants, with switch sets
 $(\{v_0\},\emptyset),(\emptyset,\{u\}),(\{v_1\},\emptyset),(\{v_0\},\emptyset)$,
 and $4=(2^{m}-1)+(2^{k}-1)$ is the absolute maximum for $(m,k)=(2,1)$.  In
 particular a BSI run can be longer than $|C|$, which the two families of
 \Cref{rem:readout} are not.
\end{enumerate}
The two guides involved are
$\val^{\tau=0}=(\tfrac{2560}{4909},\tfrac{2175}{4909},\tfrac{2400}{4909})$ with
Max best response $(1,0)$, and $\val^{\tau=1}=w^{\ast}$ with Max best response
$(0,1)$; the Min best response is $\tau=0$ at $\sigma_0$ and $\tau=1$ at every
later strategy, and the value vectors along the run are
$\val_{\sigma_0}=(0,0,0)$,
$\val_{\sigma_1}=(\tfrac{5885}{32768},\tfrac{29}{1024},\tfrac1{32})$,
$\val_{\sigma_2}=(\tfrac{191}{1024},\tfrac14,\tfrac1{32})$ and
$\val_{\sigma_3}=w^{\ast}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The rows all leak, so $W_2$ is stopping by \Cref{lem:trapchar}.  Everything
else is a finite exact computation, carried out twice: in the normal form,
with $\val_\sigma$ the componentwise minimum over both Min strategies and
$\val^{\tau}$ the componentwise maximum over all four Max strategies; and on
the realised $34$-vertex SSG with the independent core of the harness, whose
$w^{\ast}$ and whose $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$ run agree.  For (iii) and the second
half of (iv): $\val_\sigma$ strictly increases at each round with
$C_{\max}\ne\emptyset$ and $\val^{\tau}$ strictly decreases at each round with
$C_{\min}\ne\emptyset$ (\Cref{lem:switch} in $G$ and in $\bar G$), so no
$\sigma$ and no $\tau$ recurs and there are at most $2^{m}-1$ rounds of the
first kind and $2^{k}-1$ of the second, every productive round being of one
kind or the other.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What a superpolynomial family must contain]\label{rem:rbr-programme}
Collecting the two-switch bound for a constant option, \Cref{lem:clamp-reverses-once,thm:reorder,cor:clamp}:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] every Max vertex switched more than twice has two non-constant
 options, and every Min vertex whose best response reverses more than once has
 two non-constant options: the round-$15$ auditor's refutation and its exact
 Min-side twin;
\item[(b)] $R\le m+\sum_td_t$, so the guide must \emph{reorder} the options at
 Max vertices $\Omega(R)$ times in total --- changing $\tau$ is not enough,
 the change must reverse a preference;
\item[(c)] in the clamped class --- every Min vertex with a constant successor
 --- $R\le m(k+1)$ under any fixed tie-breaking preference at the Min
 vertices, which is $O(N^{2})$ and no more.  So the clamped class
 cannot carry a superpolynomial family, and the most a clamp construction can
 give is quadratic.
\end{enumerate}
Two statements would be real progress, and neither is proved here.

\medskip\noindent\textsc{Gap 1 (quadratic, clamped).} \emph{There is a family
of stopping SSGs on $O(n\log n)$ vertices with $|\Vmax|=|\Vmin|=\Theta(n)$, in
which every Min vertex has a constant successor, on which $R_{\mathrm{BR}}$
takes $\Theta(n^{2})$ rounds from a stated start.}  By (c) this is the
ceiling of the clamped class; it would be the first superlinear lower bound
for the rule, $\mathrm{RD}(n)$ giving only $\Theta(N)$.  The shape is forced
by \Cref{thm:reorder}: $k$ clamps engaging in succession give $k+1$ guides,
and each block must be a cascade of $m$ single switches, so the construction
needs a Max chain that sweeps forwards under one guide and backwards under the
next.

\medskip\noindent\textsc{Gap 2 (superpolynomial).} \emph{There is a stopping
SSG with a Min vertex $u$, both of whose options are non-constant, along whose
$R_{\mathrm{BR}}$ run the sign of
$\val_{\sigma_t}(u^{(0)})-\val_{\sigma_t}(u^{(1)})$ changes superpolynomially
often.}  By \Cref{thm:reorder} this is necessary; by \Cref{prop:leapfrog} the
analogous reversal at a \emph{Max} vertex is available at $O(1)$ vertices per
reversal, but there the reversals are driven by fresh drivers that switch once
each, and what is needed here is a leapfrog whose drivers are the run itself.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Stopping is necessary]\label{prop:bsi-nonstopping}
For the SSG on non-sinks $0,\dots,5$ with $t_0=6$, $t_1=7$, types
$(\mathrm{avg},\min,\max,\max,\mathrm{avg},\max)$ and successor pairs
$(6,7),(7,1),(7,0),(0,4),(5,0),(3,1)$, the set $\{1,5\}$ is a trap, so the
game is not stopping. At $\sigma=(2\mapsto t_1,3\mapsto0,5\mapsto1)$ and
$\tau=(1\mapsto t_1)$ one has
$\val_\sigma=(\tfrac12,0,1,\tfrac12,\tfrac14,0)$,
$w^\ast=(\tfrac12,0,1,\tfrac12,\tfrac12,\tfrac12)$ and
$\val^\tau=(\tfrac12,1,1,\tfrac34,\tfrac34,1)$, so $S_\sigma=\{5\}$ is
nonempty while $C_{\max}=C_{\min}=\emptyset$: BSI halts at a pair that is
not optimal.
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:bsi}
Three things and a caution. First, \Cref{thm:bsi-nostall} is what a
one-sided rule cannot have: \Cref{def:rule}'s rules halt when no vertex is
strictly switchable, which is a statement about $\sigma$ alone, whereas the
veto in \Cref{def:bsi} could a priori leave $S_\sigma$ nonempty and still
halt --- and the theorem says that on a stopping game this never happens:
a halt is an optimum, with $S_\sigma=S^\tau=\emptyset$ (on the
non-stopping instance of \Cref{prop:bsi-nonstopping} it does happen).
Second, no barrier in this document covers BSI: \Cref{cor:no-height}
constrains measures on the Max strategy lattice alone,
\Cref{thm:normalform-barrier} and \Cref{thm:window-barrier} cover rules
that behave as all-switches on padded games, which a rule filtering
$S_\sigma$ by $\val^\tau$ does not (\Cref{rem:bsi-br}), and
\Cref{prop:locality} is about bounded radius. Third, we know of no
superpolynomial family for it.

The caution is that symmetry by itself buys nothing, and the reason is
already in this document. Dropping the vetoes gives
$C_{\max}=S_\sigma$ and $C_{\min}=S^\tau$, and the two tracks then run
independently: unguided bidirectional improvement is exactly all-switches
on $G$ run beside all-switches on $\bar G$, and by the disjoint-union
gadget in the proof of \Cref{thm:compare-equivalence} a polynomial bound
for it is equivalent to one for all-switches. What the vetoes add is
information from the opposite track, and the natural way to quantify that
--- asking for a guide $U$ whose order at every Max vertex agrees with
$w^\ast$'s --- is target-equivalent, being the non-adaptive sufficiency of
\Cref{thm:compare-equivalence}: such a guide is exactly the $n$ comparison
bits, and $\operatorname{argmax}U$ is then already optimal by
\Cref{thm:opt-subcube}. So the content of \Cref{def:bsi} is the veto by
$\val^\tau$, which is \emph{not} such a guide, and what is open is its
round count.
\end{remark}



\subsection{The induced orientation of the two-player cube can be cyclic}

On the Max-only cube the map $\sigma\mapsto\val_\sigma$ is a strictly
increasing potential along improving switches (\Cref{lem:switch}), so
that orientation is acyclic. It is tempting to work instead on the
\emph{combined} cube of pairs $(\sigma,\tau)$, orienting each edge in the
direction its owner prefers; algorithms and lower-bound machinery for
unique-sink orientations are frequently stated under an additional
\emph{acyclicity} hypothesis. The following shows that hypothesis is
unavailable on the combined cube.

We orient the cube $\{0,1\}^{\Vmax\cup\Vmin}$ of positional profiles by
directing the edge in coordinate $v$ away from a profile exactly when
switching $v$ is improving for its owner --- increasing $v_{\sigma,\tau}(v)$
if $v\in\Vmax$, decreasing it if $v\in\Vmin$. We make no claim here about
any linear-complementarity formulation; the statement below concerns this
improving-switch orientation only.

\begin{theorem}[The combined-cube orientation can be cyclic]\label{thm:cyclic-uso}
There is a stopping game with two Max vertices $a,b$ and one Min vertex
$m$ whose induced orientation of the $3$-cube of strategy profiles is a
unique-sink orientation containing a directed $6$-cycle. (That the
profile cube of a stopping game carries a unique-sink orientation is
proved from scratch in \Cref{thm:profile-uso}; it is also Stickney and
Watson's, through the P-matrix linear complementarity formulation of
\Cref{rem:lcp}. That simultaneous switching by both players need not
converge is in Condon's analysis of algorithms for these games, from
memory, unchecked against the source. The explicit cyclic instance is
ours.) Explicitly,
taking each option to be a sub-probability distribution over
$\{a,b,m\}$ together with a weight on $t_1$ (all dyadic with denominator
$16$, hence realisable by depth-$4$ trees of average vertices):
\[
\begin{array}{llll}
  a: &\text{option }0:\ \tfrac12\!\to\!a,\ \tfrac18\!\to\!t_1;
     &\text{option }1:\ \tfrac78\!\to\!m,\ \tfrac1{16}\!\to\!t_1;\\
  b: &\text{option }0:\ \tfrac{15}{16}\!\to\!a;
     &\text{option }1:\ \tfrac12\!\to\!b,\ \tfrac1{16}\!\to\!t_1;\\
  m: &\text{option }0:\ \tfrac12\!\to\!a;
     &\text{option }1:\ \tfrac{15}{16}\!\to\!b.
\end{array}
\]
Every option has total mass at most $\tfrac{15}{16}<1$, so the game is
stopping. Orienting, at each profile $S\in\{0,1\}^{\{a,b,m\}}$, the edge
in coordinate $v$ away from $S$ exactly when switching $v$ is improving
for its owner, one obtains a USO --- all $27$ faces have a unique sink
and no comparison is tied --- whose vertex values are
\[
\begin{array}{ll}
  S=(0,0,0):\ \bigl(\tfrac14,\tfrac{15}{64},\tfrac18\bigr),\ \text{out}=\emptyset;
  & S=(0,0,1):\ \bigl(\tfrac14,\tfrac{15}{64},\tfrac{225}{1024}\bigr),\ \text{out}=\{a,m\};\\
  S=(0,1,0):\ \bigl(\tfrac14,\tfrac18,\tfrac18\bigr),\ \text{out}=\{b,m\};
  & S=(0,1,1):\ \bigl(\tfrac14,\tfrac18,\tfrac{15}{128}\bigr),\ \text{out}=\{b\};\\
  S=(1,0,0):\ \bigl(\tfrac19,\tfrac5{48},\tfrac1{18}\bigr),\ \text{out}=\{a,b\};
  & S=(1,0,1):\ \bigl(\tfrac{128}{473},\tfrac{120}{473},\tfrac{225}{946}\bigr),\ \text{out}=\{m\};\\
  S=(1,1,0):\ \bigl(\tfrac19,\tfrac18,\tfrac1{18}\bigr),\ \text{out}=\{a\};
  & S=(1,1,1):\ \bigl(\tfrac{169}{1024},\tfrac18,\tfrac{15}{128}\bigr),\ \text{out}=\{a,b,m\}.
\end{array}
\]
The six profiles other than the sink $(0,0,0)$ and the source $(1,1,1)$
form the directed cycle
\[
  (0,0,1)\to(1,0,1)\to(1,0,0)\to(1,1,0)\to(0,1,0)\to(0,1,1)\to(0,0,1),
\]
with flip sequence $a,m,b,a,m,b$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Each of the eight profiles induces a Markov chain whose value vector is
the unique solution of a $3\times3$ linear system with rational
coefficients; solving the eight systems exactly gives the table. Each of
the $24$ vertex--coordinate comparisons is then a comparison of two
explicit rationals; all $24$ are strict, so the orientation is
well defined and degeneracy does not arise. For instance at $S=(0,0,1)$
and coordinate $a$: the current value is $\tfrac14$, while option $1$
evaluates to $\tfrac1{16}+\tfrac78\cdot\tfrac{225}{1024}=\tfrac{2087}{8192}>\tfrac14$,
so the $a$-edge points away from $S$. Checking each of the $27$ faces of
the $3$-cube (the cube itself, six facets, twelve edges, eight vertices)
exhibits exactly one sink in each, so the orientation is a USO. Finally,
each of the six listed transitions flips a coordinate lying in the
out-set of its tail, so the displayed closed walk is a directed cycle.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Simultaneous switching on this instance]\label{rem:cyclic-antipodal}
The \emph{antipodal} walk $\pi\mapsto\pi\oplus s_C(\pi)$ of the profile
orientation (\Cref{def:profile-orientation}) --- both players switching at
once every vertex whose owner strictly prefers its other action, the
two-player form of all-switches --- cycles on the instance above: flipping
the printed out-sets gives $(0,0,1)\to(1,0,0)\to(0,1,0)\to(0,0,1)$, a
directed triangle, while $(1,1,1)$ goes straight to the sink and the
remaining three profiles enter the cycle, six of eight in all. So
the attribution to Condon above is now an owned instance (the round-$17$
order-lattice route, recomputed here from a $25$-vertex game ($23$ non-sinks) realising the
normal form, \texttt{scripts/round17-verify/OL3\_GAME.json}). With at most
two controlled vertices the antipodal walk cannot cycle: a cycle through
all four profiles of the square would flip one coordinate per step, leaving
every profile with an outgoing edge and the square without a sink, against
\Cref{thm:profile-uso}; a cycle of length two or three would make some
coordinate strictly switchable at both ends of one edge, or leave the
square with two sinks, against \Cref{lem:profile-trichotomy} and
\Cref{thm:profile-uso}. On a profile-nondegenerate game in which every
successor of a controlled vertex is an average vertex or a sink, the walk
is exactly what the iteration ``decode a preorder, re-sort the decoded
values'' of \Cref{rem:order-unique} becomes after one step (the decode
then compares the ranks of the two successors, which are ordered as their
values are), so that iteration cycles too. Without that separation the
iteration can cycle even with two controlled vertices, since the decode
then looks one controlled step ahead: on the ten-vertex stopping game (eight non-sinks)
$v\to(u,p_4)$, $u\to(p_2,p_7)$, $p_2\to(t_0,p_5)$, $p_3\to(p_5,p_4)$,
$p_4\to(v,p_6)$, $p_5\to(t_1,t_0)$, $p_6\to(v,t_0)$, $p_7\to(p_3,v)$ with
$v\in\Vmax$, $u\in\Vmin$, it alternates between the profiles $(1,0)$ and
$(0,1)$ while the antipodal walk converges (the route's correctness
auditor; recomputed, \texttt{scripts/round17-verify/ol\_gc.py}).
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:no-potential}
There is no real-valued potential on the profiles of a general SSG that
strictly increases along every improving edge of the two-player strategy
cube. In particular, algorithmic and lower-bound machinery that assumes
acyclic unique-sink orientations does not apply to the combined
two-player cube.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
A directed cycle forbids a strictly increasing potential; the cycle is
supplied by \Cref{thm:cyclic-uso}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
\Cref{cor:no-potential} does not contradict \Cref{thm:short-path}: the
latter concerns the \emph{Max-only} cube, on which $\sigma\mapsto\val_\sigma$
is a strictly increasing potential along improving switches by
\Cref{cor:comparison}. Acyclicity holds on each one-player cube and fails
on the combined cube.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Value iteration is exponential already without players}

Iterating $T$ from $0$ converges to $\val$ (\Cref{thm:contraction}), and
by \Cref{lem:denominator} it suffices to approximate $\val(v_0)$ to
within $1/(2D)$ to decide \Cref{prob:main}. The following family shows
that the number of iterations required is exponential, and that this has
nothing to do with the presence of players: the family has none.

\begin{theorem}[Exponential lower bound for value iteration]\label{thm:vi-lower}
For $m\ge3$ let $G_m$ be the SSG with non-sink vertices
$c_1,\dots,c_L$, $L=2m$, \emph{all} in $\Vavg$ (so
$\Vmax=\Vmin=\emptyset$), and edges
\[
  c_i\to\{o_i,\;c_{i+1}\}\ \ (1\le i\le L-1),\qquad c_L\to\{t_0,\;t_1\},
\]
where $o_i=c_1$ for $1\le i\le m-1$, $o_m=t_1$, and $o_i=t_0$ for
$m+1\le i\le 2m-1$. Then $G_m$ is stopping, has $2m$ non-sink vertices,
and
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item $\val(c_1)=\tfrac12+2^{-(m+1)}$, so $\val(c_1)>\tfrac12$ by an
  exponentially small margin;
\item $\min_i\val(c_i)=2^{-m}$;
\item $\bigl(T^{k}0\bigr)(c_1)<\tfrac12$ for every
  $k<\ln 2\cdot 2^{m-2}$.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently value iteration from $0$ requires $2^{\Omega(N)}$ iterations
before its iterate at $c_1$ can certify $\val(c_1)\ge\tfrac12$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
$G_m$ is stopping because from every $c_i$ the path
$c_i\to c_{i+1}\to\cdots\to c_L\to t_0$ has positive probability.
As there are no players, $T$ is the affine map $x\mapsto Mx+b$ with $M$
the substochastic transition matrix and $b$ the vector of one-step
probabilities of landing in $t_1$, and $\val$ is its unique fixed point.

(i) Call the segment of the trajectory from $c_1$ until the token is
first at a sink or back at $c_1$ an \emph{excursion}. The token reaches
$c_i$ without leaving earlier with probability $2^{-(i-1)}$ and then
exits to $o_i$ with probability $\tfrac12$, so it exits at $o_i$ with
probability $2^{-i}$ for $i\le L-1$, and reaches each of $t_0,t_1$ from
$c_L$ with probability $2^{-L}$. Hence the excursion ends at $t_1$ with
probability $p=2^{-m}+2^{-2m}$, at $t_0$ with probability
$q=\sum_{i=m+1}^{2m-1}2^{-i}+2^{-2m}=2^{-m}-2^{-2m}$, and returns to
$c_1$ with probability $\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}2^{-i}=1-2^{-(m-1)}$; these sum
to $1$. By the strong Markov property
$\val(c_1)=p+(1-p-q)\val(c_1)$, so
$\val(c_1)=p/(p+q)=(2^{-m}+2^{-2m})/2^{-m+1}=\tfrac12+2^{-(m+1)}$.

(ii) For $i\ge m+1$, $\val(c_i)=\tfrac12\val(c_{i+1})$ with
$\val(c_L)=\tfrac12$, giving $\val(c_i)=2^{-(2m-i+1)}$, least at $i=m+1$
where it equals $2^{-m}$. For $i\le m-1$,
$\val(c_i)=\tfrac12\val(c_1)+\tfrac12\val(c_{i+1})\ge\tfrac14$, and
$\val(c_m)=\tfrac12+\tfrac12\val(c_{m+1})$. Hence the minimum is
$2^{-m}$.

(iii) Let $e^{(k)}:=\val-T^{k}0$. Since $T$ is affine with fixed point
$\val$, $e^{(k)}=M^{k}\val$, and as $M\ge0$ and $\val\ge0$,
\[
  e^{(k)}(c_1)\;=\;\mathbb{E}_{c_1}\bigl[\val(X_k)\mathbf{1}\{X_k\ \text{not absorbed}\}\bigr]
  \;\ge\;\bigl(\min_i\val(c_i)\bigr)\Pr\nolimits_{c_1}[\text{alive at }k]
  \;\ge\;2^{-m}\bigl(1-2^{1-m}\bigr)^{k},
\]
the last step because each excursion is absorbed with probability
$p+q=2^{1-m}$, independently across excursions, and each excursion takes
at least one step, so surviving $k$ excursions implies surviving $k$
steps. Now $\val(c_1)-\tfrac12=2^{-(m+1)}$, so
$\bigl(T^{k}0\bigr)(c_1)<\tfrac12$ whenever
$e^{(k)}(c_1)>2^{-(m+1)}$, i.e.\ whenever $(1-2^{1-m})^{k}>\tfrac12$.
Since $-\ln(1-u)\le 2u$ for $0<u\le\tfrac12$, taking $u=2^{1-m}$
(legitimate for $m\ge3$) this holds for every
$k<\ln2/(2\cdot2^{1-m})=\ln2\cdot2^{m-2}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Exact rational computation confirms
$\val(c_1)=\tfrac12+2^{-(m+1)}$ and $\min_i\val(c_i)=2^{-m}$ for
$m=3,\dots,12$, and the number of iterations before
$\bigl(T^{k}0\bigr)(c_1)$ first reaches $\tfrac12$ is measured to be
$13,37,99,248,595,1385,3149,7041,15545,33985$ for those $m$ --- the ratio
of consecutive counts decreasing from $2.85$ towards $2$, comfortably
above the proved bound $\ln2\cdot 2^{m-2}$. (That value iteration can be
exponentially slow on such games is Condon's observation, from memory,
unchecked against the source; the player-free family and the exact counts
are ours.)

Two consequences deserve emphasis. First, the obstruction is not the
\emph{precision} of the arithmetic but the \emph{mixing time} of the
chain: the margin $\val(c_1)-\tfrac12=2^{-(m+1)}$ is exponentially small,
so no fixed polynomial number of iterations at any precision can decide
the instance. Second, since $G_m$ has no Max or Min vertices at all, the
failure of value iteration is not caused by the interaction of the two
players; it is already present in the one-dimensional problem of
computing an absorption probability. Any polynomial algorithm must
therefore compute values by solving the linear system exactly rather than
by iterating $T$.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Improving switches need not point toward the optimal set}

By \Cref{thm:opt-subcube}, in a stopping game $\Opt=\prod_{v}A_v$ where
$A_v\subseteq\{0,1\}$ is the set of choices at $v$ attaining
$\max\bigl(w^\ast(v^{(0)}),w^\ast(v^{(1)})\bigr)$. This makes the
Hamming distance to the optimal set explicit:
\[
  d(\sigma)\;:=\;\bigl|\{v\in\Vmax : \sigma(v)\notin A_v\}\bigr|
  \;=\;\min_{\sigma^\ast\in\Opt}\bigl|\{v:\sigma(v)\ne\sigma^\ast(v)\}\bigr| .
\]
If every strictly switchable vertex were a vertex at which $\sigma$ is
\emph{wrong}, i.e.\ if $S_\sigma\subseteq\{v:\sigma(v)\notin A_v\}$ always
held, then \emph{every} single improving switch would decrease $d$ by
one, and strategy iteration under \emph{any} selection rule would
terminate within $n$ steps --- giving a polynomial algorithm. It does
not hold.

\begin{theorem}[No naive Hamming potential]\label{thm:hamming-refuted}
There is a stopping SSG with two Max vertices, no Min vertices and no
average vertices, and a Max strategy $\sigma$, such that some
$v\in S_\sigma$ satisfies $\sigma(v)\in A_v$; switching $v$ is strictly
improving yet leaves $d(\sigma)$ unchanged. Explicitly, let
$\Vmax=\{p,q\}$ with
\[
  p\to\{t_1,\,t_0\},\qquad q\to\{t_1,\,p\},
\]
and let $\sigma$ be $p\mapsto t_0$, $q\mapsto p$. Then $w^\ast=(1,1)$,
$A_p=\{t_1\}$, $A_q=\{t_1,p\}$, $\Opt=\{p\mapsto t_1\}\times\{t_1,p\}$,
and $\val_\sigma=(0,0)$. Both $p$ and $q$ are strictly switchable at
$\sigma$, but $\sigma(q)=p\in A_q$, so switching $q$ yields
$\val=(0,1)$ --- a strict improvement --- while $d$ remains $1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The game is stopping: $p$ moves to a sink in one step under either
choice, and $q$ moves to a sink or to $p$. Its value is $w^\ast=(1,1)$,
since Max may play $p\to t_1$ and $q\to t_1$. Hence
$w^\ast(p)=w^\ast(t_1)=1$, so both choices at $q$ attain the maximum and
$A_q=\{t_1,p\}$, while $w^\ast(t_1)=1>0=w^\ast(t_0)$ gives
$A_p=\{t_1\}$. Under $\sigma$ the token goes $q\to p\to t_0$, so
$\val_\sigma=(0,0)$; both switches are strictly improving because
$\val_\sigma(t_1)=1>0$. Finally $d(\sigma)=1$ (only $p$ is wrong) and
$d(\sigma[q])=1$ as well.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:hamming}
No proof of a polynomial iteration bound can proceed by arguing that an
arbitrary improving switch decreases the Hamming distance to $\Opt$. Any
Hamming-based argument must additionally explain \emph{which} switch to
take, which is the selection problem of \Cref{cor:selection}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Immediate from \Cref{thm:hamming-refuted}: at the strategy exhibited
there the improving switch at $q$ does not reduce $d$, so $d$ is not
decreased by every improving switch.
\end{proof}

One might hope that although \emph{some} improving switch fails to be
wrong, a specific polynomial-time rule always selects a wrong one; by
\Cref{thm:short-path} a wrong switchable vertex always exists, so such a
rule would terminate in at most $n$ iterations and settle
\Cref{prob:main}. The following shows that the natural candidates do not
have this property.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:rules-fail}
None of the following selection rules always chooses a wrong vertex from
$S_\sigma$: largest improvement gap
$\val_\sigma(\bar\sigma(v))-\val_\sigma(\sigma(v))$; smallest such gap;
largest $\val_\sigma(\bar\sigma(v))$; smallest $\val_\sigma(\sigma(v))$;
first or last index; and the one-step lookahead rule maximising
$\Phi(\val_{\sigma[v]})$, where $\Phi(x)=\sum_u x(u)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A single stopping game refutes the lookahead rule, the strongest of the
seven. Let $\Vmax=\{u_0,u_1,u_3,u_4\}$, $\Vavg=\{u_2\}$, with
\[
  u_0\to\{t_0,t_1\},\quad u_1\to\{t_0,u_0\},\quad u_2\to\{u_4,t_1\},\quad
  u_3\to\{t_0,u_1\},\quad u_4\to\{u_3,u_0\}.
\]
Then $w^\ast\equiv1$, so the greedy sets are $A_{u_0}=A_{u_1}=A_{u_3}=$
the second listed successor, while $A_{u_4}=\{u_3,u_0\}$ contains
\emph{both} choices (both have $w^\ast=1$); hence $u_4$ is never wrong.
Take $\sigma$ given by $u_0\mapsto t_1$, $u_1\mapsto t_0$,
$u_3\mapsto t_0$, $u_4\mapsto u_3$. Exact computation gives
$\val_\sigma=(1,0,\tfrac12,0,0)$ on $(u_0,\dots,u_4)$. Exactly two
vertices are strictly switchable: $u_1$, which is wrong, with
$\Phi(\val_{\sigma[u_1]})=\tfrac72$; and $u_4$, which is not wrong, with
$\Phi(\val_{\sigma[u_4]})=4$. The lookahead rule therefore selects
$u_4$, which is not wrong. The remaining six rules are refuted by the
following one-player stopping games, each verified in exact arithmetic;
in each, $w^\ast$ is as stated, a vertex is \emph{not wrong} because both
its successors have the same $w^\ast$, and the rule's unique choice is
such a vertex.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2.2em]
\item \emph{Largest gap.} $\Vmax=\{u_0,u_2\}$, $\Vavg=\{u_1\}$,
  $u_0\to\{t_1,u_2\}$, $u_1\to\{t_1,u_0\}$, $u_2\to\{u_1,t_0\}$;
  $w^\ast\equiv1$; $\sigma$: $u_0\mapsto u_2$, $u_2\mapsto t_0$;
  $\val_\sigma=(0,\tfrac12,0)$; the gaps are $1$ at $u_0$ and $\tfrac12$
  at $u_2$, and $u_0$ is not wrong.
\item \emph{Smallest gap.} Same kinds; $u_0\to\{u_1,t_1\}$,
  $u_1\to\{t_1,u_2\}$, $u_2\to\{t_0,t_1\}$; $w^\ast\equiv1$; $\sigma$:
  $u_0\mapsto u_1$, $u_2\mapsto t_0$; $\val_\sigma=(\tfrac12,\tfrac12,0)$;
  gaps $\tfrac12$ at $u_0$ and $1$ at $u_2$; $u_0$ is not wrong.
\item \emph{Largest $\val_\sigma(\bar\sigma(v))$.} Same kinds;
  $u_0\to\{t_0,u_1\}$, $u_1\to\{u_0,t_1\}$, $u_2\to\{u_1,t_1\}$;
  $w^\ast\equiv1$; $\sigma$: $u_0\mapsto t_0$, $u_2\mapsto u_1$;
  $\val_\sigma=(0,\tfrac12,\tfrac12)$; the alternatives are worth
  $\tfrac12$ at $u_0$ and $1$ at $u_2$; $u_2$ is not wrong.
\item \emph{Smallest $\val_\sigma(\sigma(v))$.} $\Vavg=\{u_0,u_1,u_2,u_3\}$,
  $\Vmax=\{u_4,u_5\}$, $u_0\to\{u_4,t_0\}$, $u_1\to\{u_2,t_0\}$,
  $u_2\to\{t_1,u_1\}$, $u_3\to\{u_3,u_5\}$, $u_4\to\{u_2,u_1\}$,
  $u_5\to\{u_0,u_1\}$;
  $w^\ast=(\tfrac13,\tfrac13,\tfrac23,\tfrac13,\tfrac23,\tfrac13)$;
  $\sigma$: $u_4\mapsto u_1$, $u_5\mapsto u_0$;
  $\val_\sigma=(\tfrac16,\tfrac13,\tfrac23,\tfrac16,\tfrac13,\tfrac16)$;
  the current values are $\tfrac13$ at $u_4$ and $\tfrac16$ at $u_5$, and
  $u_5$ is not wrong.
\item \emph{First index.} $\Vavg=\{u_0\}$, $\Vmax=\{u_1,u_2\}$,
  $u_0\to\{u_2,t_0\}$, $u_1\to\{t_1,u_2\}$, $u_2\to\{t_1,t_0\}$;
  $w^\ast=(\tfrac12,1,1)$; $\sigma$: $u_1\mapsto u_2$, $u_2\mapsto t_0$;
  $\val_\sigma\equiv0$; both Max vertices are switchable and $u_1$ is
  not wrong.
\item \emph{Last index.} Same kinds; $u_0\to\{u_1,t_0\}$,
  $u_1\to\{t_1,u_0\}$, $u_2\to\{u_1,t_1\}$; $w^\ast=(\tfrac12,1,1)$;
  $\sigma$: $u_1\mapsto u_0$, $u_2\mapsto u_1$; $\val_\sigma\equiv0$; both
  are switchable and $u_2$ is not wrong.
\end{itemize}
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Wrong vertices can be as rare as possible]\label{prop:needle}
For every $k\ge2$ there is a stopping SSG with exactly $k$ non-sink
vertices, all of them Max vertices, and a Max strategy $\sigma$ such that
$S_\sigma=\Vmax$ --- every Max vertex is strictly switchable --- while
exactly one vertex is wrong. Explicitly, let
$\Vmax=\{p,u_1,\dots,u_{k-1}\}$, $\Vmin=\Vavg=\emptyset$, and
\[
  p\to\{t_1,\,t_0\},\qquad u_i\to\{t_1,\,p\}\quad(1\le i\le k-1),
\]
and let $\sigma$ take the second listed successor everywhere, i.e.\
$\sigma(p)=t_0$ and $\sigma(u_i)=p$. Then $|S_\sigma|=k$ and the unique
wrong vertex is $p$. Hence a uniformly random element of $S_\sigma$ is
wrong with probability exactly $1/k$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The game is stopping: from $p$ a sink is reached in one step under either
choice, and each $u_i$ moves either to $t_1$ or to $p$, so every play
reaches a sink within two steps.

Its value is $w^\ast\equiv1$: Max plays $p\mapsto t_1$, giving
$w^\ast(p)=1$, and then $w^\ast(u_i)=\max(1,w^\ast(p))=1$. Consequently
$A_p=\{t_1\}$, because $w^\ast(t_1)=1>0=w^\ast(t_0)$, while
$A_{u_i}=\{t_1,p\}$ contains \emph{both} successors, because
$w^\ast(t_1)=1=w^\ast(p)$.

Under $\sigma$ the token goes $u_i\to p\to t_0$, so $\val_\sigma\equiv0$.
Every vertex is therefore strictly switchable: at $p$ we have
$\val_\sigma(t_1)=1>0=\val_\sigma(t_0)$, and at $u_i$ we have
$\val_\sigma(t_1)=1>0=\val_\sigma(p)$. Thus $S_\sigma=\Vmax$ and
$|S_\sigma|=k$. Finally $\sigma(p)=t_0\notin A_p$, so $p$ is wrong, while
$\sigma(u_i)=p\in A_{u_i}$, so no $u_i$ is wrong.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
\Cref{prop:needle} is the exact quantitative counterpart of
\Cref{thm:short-path}. That theorem guarantees at least one wrong
switchable vertex; this family shows the guarantee cannot be improved,
since the wrong vertices can be a $1/k$ fraction of $S_\sigma$ with
$k=|\Vmax|$. Choosing a strictly switchable vertex uniformly at random
therefore locates a wrong one with probability only $1/|\Vmax|$ in the
worst case. Note also that every vertex here \emph{is} improving to
switch --- the vertices $u_i$ are not mistakes, they simply fail to reduce
the distance to $\Opt$ --- so no rule can be salvaged by first discarding
non-improving candidates.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
\Cref{prop:rules-fail} does not show that these rules require
superpolynomially many iterations; it shows only that the particular
$n$-step argument available from \Cref{thm:short-path} cannot be
completed with them. The distinction matters: the length of improving
paths is provably linear (\Cref{cor:selection}), so any superpolynomial
behaviour must come from a rule repeatedly failing to find the short
path, not from its absence.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The objective of the strategy-space formulation is
