unstructured}\label{sec:submodular}

By \Cref{thm:determinacy} the value is $\min_{\tau}\val^{\tau}(v_0)$ over
positional Min strategies, where $\val^{\tau}$ --- the value when Min is frozen
to $\tau$ and Max plays optimally --- is computable in polynomial time by
\Cref{thm:one-player}, applied to the game in which each Min vertex is retyped
as an average vertex with both edges to $\tau(v)$. So \Cref{prob:main} is the
minimisation of a polynomial-time computable function over the cube
$\{0,1\}^{\Vmin}$, and there is one general hammer for such a problem:
a submodular function on $\{0,1\}^{k}$ is minimised in polynomial time.
Submodularity is not invariant under flipping a coordinate, so the honest
question is whether \emph{some} orientation makes
$\tau\mapsto\val^{\tau}(v_0)$ submodular. None does, and the reason is that the
objective already ranges over every read-once formula on deterministic games.

\begin{lemma}[Read-once realisation]\label{lem:readonce}
Let $F$ be built from the constants $0,1$ by the operations
$\mathrm{OR}(A,B)$ and $\mathrm{MUX}_i(A,B)$, each index $i\in\{1,\dots,k\}$
used at most once, and let $|F|$ be its number of operations. There is a
stopping SSG $G_F$ with $\Vavg=\emptyset$, at most $k$ Min vertices ---
one $m_i$ for each index $i$ that $F$ uses --- at most $|F|$ Max vertices, $|V(G_F)|=|F|+2$, and a vertex $r$
with
\[
  \val^{\tau}(r)\;=\;F(\tau)\qquad\text{for every positional Min strategy }\tau,
\]
where $\tau_i=0$ means that $m_i$ takes its first successor and
$\mathrm{MUX}_i(A,B)$ evaluates to $A$ if $\tau_i=0$ and to $B$ otherwise.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Realise the parse tree: a constant by the corresponding sink, an
$\mathrm{OR}$ node by a Max vertex whose two successors are its two arguments,
and a $\mathrm{MUX}_i$ node by the Min vertex $m_i$ with the same two
successors. The digraph is a tree oriented towards the sinks, hence acyclic, so
no nonempty set can meet the condition of \Cref{lem:trapchar} and $G_F$ is
stopping. Fixing $\tau$ deletes one out-edge at each Min vertex; in the
resulting one-player game every value is $0$ or $1$, and the value at $u$ is $1$
exactly when Max can reach $t_1$ from $u$. That reachability predicate satisfies
$\mathrm{reach}(u)=\mathrm{reach}(a)\vee\mathrm{reach}(b)$ at a Max vertex
$u\to(a,b)$ and $\mathrm{reach}(m_i)=\mathrm{reach}$ of the $\tau_i$-successor
at a Min vertex, which is exactly the evaluation of $F$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[No orientation makes the objective
submodular]\label{prop:no-submodular}
Let $G$ be the stopping SSG on five vertices with
$\Vmin=\{m_1,m_2,m_3\}$, $\Vmax=\Vavg=\emptyset$ and
\[
  m_1\to(m_2,t_0),\qquad m_2\to(m_3,t_0),\qquad m_3\to(t_1,t_0).
\]
Then $\val^{\tau}(m_1)=1$ if $\tau=(0,0,0)$ and $0$ otherwise, and for no
$c\in\{0,1\}^{3}$ is $x\mapsto\val^{x\oplus c}(m_1)$ submodular. Dually, in the
stopping game with $\Vmax=\{v_1,v_2,v_3\}$, $\Vmin=\Vavg=\emptyset$ and
$v_1\to(v_2,t_1)$, $v_2\to(v_3,t_1)$, $v_3\to(t_0,t_1)$, one has
$\val_{\sigma}(v_1)=1-[\sigma=(0,0,0)]$, and for no $c$ is
$x\mapsto\val_{x\oplus c}(v_1)$ supermodular.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For the first game, $\val^{\tau}(m_3)=[\tau_3=0]$,
$\val^{\tau}(m_2)=[\tau_2=0]\,\val^{\tau}(m_3)$ and
$\val^{\tau}(m_1)=[\tau_1=0]\,\val^{\tau}(m_2)$, which is
\Cref{lem:readonce} for
$F=\mathrm{MUX}_1(\mathrm{MUX}_2(\mathrm{MUX}_3(1,0),0),0)$.

Now let $f$ be the indicator of a single point $p\in\{0,1\}^{3}$. Submodularity
demands $f(x{+}e_i)+f(x{+}e_j)\ge f(x)+f(x{+}e_i{+}e_j)$ whenever
$x_i=x_j=0$ and $i\ne j$. If $p$ has at least two coordinates equal to $1$, say
$p_i=p_j=1$, take $x:=p-e_i-e_j$; then the left side is $0$ and the right side
is $f(p)=1$. If $p$ has at most one coordinate equal to $1$, take $x:=p$ and
$i,j$ any two coordinates outside the support of $p$, which exist because
$3-|p|\ge2$; then again the left side is $0$ and the right side is $1$. So no
indicator of a point of $\{0,1\}^{3}$ is submodular. Replacing $f$ by
$x\mapsto f(x\oplus c)$ replaces $p$ by $p\oplus c$, so this covers every
orientation.

For the second game $\val_{\sigma}(v_3)=[\sigma_3=1]$,
$\val_{\sigma}(v_2)=\max([\sigma_2=1],\val_\sigma(v_3))$ and
$\val_{\sigma}(v_1)=\max([\sigma_1=1],\val_\sigma(v_2))$, so
$\val_{\sigma}(v_1)=1-[\sigma=(0,0,0)]$; and $1-f$ is supermodular exactly when
$f$ is submodular.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:no-submodular}
Three points.

The failure is not a matter of degree, and codimension three is where it starts.
The indicator of a codimension-$2$ subcube \emph{is} submodular, after flipping
exactly one of its two relevant coordinates; we checked all four orientations in
that case and all eight in the case above. Since \Cref{lem:readonce} realises
every read-once formula, any property of the objective that some read-once
formula lacks is unavailable, and submodularity-in-a-suitable-orientation is
such a property.

The witnesses are not hard instances: they have no average vertices and are
solved by one reachability sweep. That is not a weakness of the refutation but
its sharpest form. The hypothesis being refuted is a statement about
\emph{every} input, so any input refutes it, and an input that no promise can
exclude refutes it most economically. What survives is the requirement that a
discrete-convexity argument read the game itself and not merely the values of
the objective.

The Max side is the dual and is closed with it. Maximising $\val_\sigma$ over
$\{0,1\}^{\Vmax}$ is polynomial when $\val_\sigma$ is supermodular, and
\Cref{prop:no-submodular} refutes that in every orientation too. These two
formulations are the natural way to beat the $2^{|\Vmin|}$ and $2^{|\Vmax|}$
enumerations that stand behind Hoffman--Karp iteration, and after
\Cref{prop:no-submodular} neither is available.

Nor does anything depend on the witness being deterministic or on its values
being $0$ and $1$. Replacing the sink $t_1$ at the end of the chain by a
subgame of value $w$ and the sinks $t_0$ by one of value $z<w$ gives
$\val^{\tau}(m_1)=z+(w-z)\,[\tau=(0,0,0)]$ in the three chain coordinates;
submodularity is preserved by increasing affine maps and inherited by faces, so
the refutation survives verbatim, and it survives the presence of further Min
vertices on which the objective does not depend. Concretely, take the
nine-vertex game with $m_1,m_2,m_3,y_2\in\Vmin$, $w_2\in\Vmax$,
$w_1,y_1\in\Vavg$ and
\[
  \begin{aligned}
  &m_1\to(m_2,t_0),\quad m_2\to(m_3,t_0),\quad m_3\to(w_1,t_0),\\
  &w_1\to(t_0,w_2),\quad w_2\to(w_1,t_1),\quad
   y_1\to(t_1,y_2),\quad y_2\to(y_1,t_0).
  \end{aligned}
\]
It is stopping, with $w^{\ast}=(0,0,0,\tfrac12,1,\tfrac12,0)$; the cycle
$w_1w_2$ carries an average and a Max vertex and the cycle $y_1y_2$ an average
and a Min vertex, so no colour is absent from every cycle and the game lies
outside \Cref{thm:kacyclic} and everything that theorem contains. Its objective
is $\val^{\tau}(m_1)=\tfrac12\,[\tau_{m_1}=\tau_{m_2}=\tau_{m_3}=0]$, and none
of the sixteen orientations of its four-dimensional Min cube makes it
submodular. We claim nothing about the difficulty of that instance; only that no
structural promise which excludes the five-vertex chain excludes it either.
\end{remark}

\section{The selection problem is the whole problem}\label{sec:selection}

\Cref{thm:opt-subcube} makes the information needed to solve a stopping
game completely explicit: an optimal strategy is determined by the $n$
bits $\bigl[\,w^\ast(v^{(0)})\ge w^\ast(v^{(1)})\,\bigr]$, one per Max
vertex. We record that these bits are individually exactly as hard as
\Cref{prob:main}, and jointly sufficient.

\begin{definition}
\textsc{SSG-Compare} is the problem: given a \emph{stopping} SSG $G$ and
two vertices $a,b$, decide whether $\val(a)\ge\val(b)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Duality]\label{lem:duality}
Let $G$ be a stopping SSG and let $\bar G$ be obtained from $G$ by
exchanging the two sinks and exchanging the roles of the players, i.e.\
$\Vmax(\bar G)=\Vmin(G)$, $\Vmin(\bar G)=\Vmax(G)$, $\Vavg$ unchanged,
and every edge into $t_1$ redirected to $t_0$ and conversely. Then
$\val_{\bar G}(v)=1-\val_G(v)$ for every \emph{non-sink} $v$. More
generally, for every Max strategy $\sigma$ and every Min strategy $\tau$
of $G$, read in $\bar G$ as a Min and a Max strategy respectively,
$\val^{\sigma}_{\bar G}=1-\val_{G,\sigma}$ and
$\val_{\bar G,\tau}=1-\val^{\tau}_{G}$ off the sinks: the proof gives the
identity profile by profile, and each frozen statement is the extremum
over the other player's strategies, which exists by
\Cref{thm:stopping-determinacy}.

The restriction to non-sinks is not cosmetic: the two games share the
sinks and their meaning, so $\val_{\bar G}(t_1)=1=\val_G(t_1)$, and the
identity fails there. Every use of the lemma below therefore takes care
that its argument is not a sink.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $G$ is stopping, under every positional pair the token is absorbed
almost surely, so the probabilities of reaching $t_1$ and of reaching
$t_0$ sum to $1$. A pair $(\sigma,\tau)$ of $G$ is the pair
$(\tau,\sigma)$ of $\bar G$ with the roles exchanged, and the $\bar
G$-payoff of that pair at $v$ is $1-v_{\sigma,\tau}(v)$. Hence, using
\Cref{thm:determinacy} twice,
\[
  \val_{\bar G}(v)=\max_\tau\min_\sigma\bigl(1-v_{\sigma,\tau}(v)\bigr)
  =1-\min_\tau\max_\sigma v_{\sigma,\tau}(v)=1-\val_G(v). \qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Equivalence]\label{thm:compare-equivalence}
\textsc{SSG-Value} and \textsc{SSG-Compare} are polynomial-time
equivalent. Moreover $n$ \emph{non-adaptive} queries to
\textsc{SSG-Compare} suffice to compute an optimal Max strategy, and
hence the exact value vector, of a stopping SSG.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\emph{\textsc{SSG-Value} $\le$ \textsc{SSG-Compare}.} Let $G$ be an
arbitrary SSG with start vertex $v_0$, and let $G_m$ and
$D=2^{\lceil 3a/2\rceil}$ be as in \Cref{thm:stopping-transform}. One
cannot simply compare $\val_{G_m}(v_0)$ against $\tfrac12$: the
transformation moves the threshold, and comparing at $\tfrac12$ gives the
wrong answer already for the one-vertex game $c\to\{t_1,t_0\}$, where
$\val_G(c)=\tfrac12$ but $\val_{G_m}(c)<\tfrac12$. We must therefore
compare against the shifted threshold, and to do so we manufacture a
vertex whose value is exactly that threshold.

Put
\[
  \theta\;:=\;\tfrac12-\tfrac{3}{8D},
\]
a dyadic rational with denominator $8D=2^{k}$, $k:=\lceil 3a/2\rceil+3$.
Write $\theta=\sum_{i=1}^{k}b_i2^{-i}$ in binary and adjoin to $G_m$ a
chain of $k$ fresh average vertices $c_1,\dots,c_k$ with
\[
  c_i\to\{\,z_i,\;c_{i+1}\,\}\ (i<k),\qquad c_k\to\{z_k,\,t_0\},
  \qquad z_i:=\begin{cases}t_1,&b_i=1\\ t_0,&b_i=0.\end{cases}
\]
Then $\val(c_i)=\tfrac12 [b_i]+\tfrac12\val(c_{i+1})$ with
$\val(c_{k+1}):=0$, so $\val(c_1)=\sum_i b_i2^{-i}=\theta$. The chain
reaches a sink from every $c_i$, no vertex of $G_m$ reaches it, and $k=O(a)$,
so the enlarged game is stopping, has polynomially many vertices, and all
original values are unchanged.

We claim $\val_G(v_0)\ge\tfrac12$ if and only if
$\val_{G_m}(v_0)\ge\val(c_1)$, a single \textsc{SSG-Compare} query. If
$\val_G(v_0)\ge\tfrac12$ then \Cref{thm:stopping-transform} gives
$\val_{G_m}(v_0)>\tfrac12-\tfrac1{4D}=\tfrac12-\tfrac2{8D}>\theta$. If
$\val_G(v_0)<\tfrac12$ then, $\val_G(v_0)$ being a rational of
denominator at most $D$, we get $\val_G(v_0)\le\tfrac12-\tfrac1{2D}$, and
Step~1 of \Cref{thm:stopping-transform} gives
$\val_{G_m}(v_0)\le\val_G(v_0)\le\tfrac12-\tfrac4{8D}<\theta$.

\emph{\textsc{SSG-Compare} $\le$ \textsc{SSG-Value}.} Let $G$ be stopping
with vertices $a,b$. Form the disjoint union of $G$ and its dual
$\bar G$ of \Cref{lem:duality}, sharing the two sinks, and adjoin a fresh
average start vertex $s$ with
\[
  s\;\to\;\{\,a,\ \bar b\,\},
\]
where $\bar b$ is the copy of $b$ in $\bar G$. We may assume $b$ is not a
sink: otherwise replace it by a fresh average vertex $b'$ with both edges
to $b$, which leaves every value unchanged, keeps the game stopping, and
has $\val(b')=\val(b)$. This is exactly the hypothesis
\Cref{lem:duality} requires, and it is not a corner case --- successors
that are sinks are common, and comparing against one directly gives the
wrong answer, since $\val_{\bar G}(t_1)=1$ rather than $0$. The union is
stopping and has $2N-1$ vertices when no fresh vertex $b'$ was needed, and
$2N+1$ otherwise (the two sinks are shared); the two components do not interact, so their values
are unchanged. By \Cref{lem:duality},
\[
  \val(s)\;=\;\tfrac12\bigl(\val_G(a)+\val_{\bar G}(\bar b)\bigr)
  \;=\;\tfrac12\bigl(1+\val_G(a)-\val_G(b)\bigr),
\]
whence $\val(s)\ge\tfrac12$ if and only if $\val_G(a)\ge\val_G(b)$: a
single \textsc{SSG-Value} query.

\emph{Non-adaptive sufficiency.} Let $G$ be stopping. For each
$v\in\Vmax$ issue the query ``$\val(v^{(0)})\ge\val(v^{(1)})$?''; these
$n$ queries do not depend on one another. Their answers determine, for
each $v$, a successor attaining
$\max\bigl(w^\ast(v^{(0)}),w^\ast(v^{(1)})\bigr)$; let $\sigma$ choose it.
By \Cref{thm:opt-subcube}, $\sigma\in\Opt$. Then $\val_\sigma=w^\ast$ is
computed in polynomial time by the linear program
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lp}
\begin{aligned}
  \text{maximise } &\textstyle\sum_{u\in V} x(u)\quad\text{subject to } x\in[0,1]^V,\ x(t_1)=1,\ x(t_0)=0,\\
  &x(v)=x(\sigma(v)) && (v\in\Vmax),\\
  &x(v)=\tfrac12\bigl(x(v^{(0)})+x(v^{(1)})\bigr) && (v\in\Vavg),\\
  &x(v)\le x(v^{(0)}),\quad x(v)\le x(v^{(1)}) && (v\in\Vmin).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Its feasible set is contained in $\{x\in\mathcal{X}:x\le T_\sigma x\}$
--- properly so in general, since the Max and average rows are equalities
--- and by \Cref{cor:comparison} every feasible $x$ satisfies
$x\le\val_\sigma$, while $\val_\sigma$ is itself feasible. Hence
$\val_\sigma$ is the unique \emph{maximiser}, coordinate by coordinate and
therefore also for the sum. The direction matters when Min is present:
the minimum of the same program can then lie far below $\val_\sigma$,
whereas with $\Vmin=\emptyset$ the equalities pin $x$ and the two
coincide.
\end{proof}

The number of bits actually needed can be reduced further: they need not
be indexed by the Max vertices at all, but by the \emph{average} vertices,
and only their relative order matters.

\begin{theorem}[The order of the average values determines the game]\label{thm:order-determines}
Let $G$ be a stopping SSG with $a=|\Vavg|$. The total preorder induced by
$w^\ast$ on $\Vavg\cup\{t_0,t_1\}$ determines $w^\ast$ completely, and
$w^\ast$ is computable from that preorder in polynomial time.
Consequently \textsc{SSG-Value} admits a certificate of
$O((a+2)\log(a+2))$ bits, and $O((a+2)\log(a+2))$ adaptive
\textsc{SSG-Compare} queries suffice to solve the game --- queries between
average vertices, and against the sinks, the latter convertible into the
former by splicing in $t_1'\to(t_1,t_1)$ and $t_0'\to(t_0,t_0)$ at a cost
of two average vertices, as in \Cref{prop:no-halving}. The bound involves
neither $|V|$ nor $|\Vmax|$ nor $|\Vmin|$; the $+2$ is not cosmetic, since
at $a=1$ the single average vertex may have value $0$, $\tfrac12$ or $1$
and one query is genuinely needed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Declare every average vertex a terminal. What remains is a deterministic
max/min game $H$ whose terminal set is $\Vavg\cup\{t_0,t_1\}$, and whose
terminal payoffs, though unknown, are ordered by the given preorder. Let
$K_0\succ K_1\succ\cdots\succ K_\ell$ be the classes, highest first, and
put $Y_i:=K_0\cup\cdots\cup K_i$.

By \Cref{lem:det-game} applied to $H$, the value of a non-terminal $v$ is
the payoff of the best class Max can force, so $v$ belongs to class $K_i$
for the least $i$ with $v\in\mathrm{Attr}(Y_i)$; and if $v$ lies in no
$\mathrm{Attr}(Y_i)$ then Min keeps the play away from every terminal
forever and $v$ has value $0$, the value of $t_0$. Each
$\mathrm{Attr}(Y_i)$ is one backward sweep, so $\ell+1\le a+2$ sweeps
assign every vertex of $G$ a class, using only the preorder.

Every vertex now carries a class, so at each $v\in\Vmax$ we may select a
successor of highest class and at each $v\in\Vmin$ one of lowest class;
let $(\sigma,\tau)$ be any such pair. Because classes order values,
$\sigma$ is greedy for $w^\ast$ and $\tau$ is greedy for Min, whence
$(T_{\sigma,\tau}w^\ast)(v)=(Tw^\ast)(v)=w^\ast(v)$ at every vertex: at
$\Vmax$ and $\Vmin$ by the choice of $\sigma,\tau$, and at $\Vavg$ and the
sinks because the operators agree there. So $w^\ast$ is a fixed point of
$T_{\sigma,\tau}$ and therefore equals $v_{\sigma,\tau}$ by
\Cref{thm:contraction} --- one exact linear solve.

A total preorder on the $a+2$ elements of $\Vavg\cup\{t_0,t_1\}$ is
specified by $O((a+2)\log(a+2))$ bits, and sorting those $a+2$ elements by
value takes $O((a+2)\log(a+2))$ comparisons, each an \textsc{SSG-Compare}
query between two of them; a query involving a sink becomes a query
between two average vertices after the splice of the statement, which
changes neither the values nor the preorder on the original vertices. The
sinks cannot simply be placed at the extremes: an average vertex may tie
with $t_1$ or with $t_0$, and the decoder needs to know it.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
\Cref{thm:order-determines} strictly sharpens the counting in
\Cref{thm:compare-equivalence} whenever $a<n$: the game is pinned down not
by one bit per Max vertex but by the relative order of the average values,
and the Max and Min vertices contribute nothing beyond what attractor
computations recover for free. Two things it does \emph{not} give. It does
not give a better algorithm --- enumerating preorders costs
$2^{\Theta(a\log a)}$, worse than the $2^{a}\mathrm{poly}(N)$ of
\Cref{thm:few-avg}. And it does not shrink the difficulty: by
\Cref{thm:compare-equivalence} each of the $O((a+2)\log(a+2))$ comparisons
is itself an instance of \Cref{prob:main}. The decoding procedure was
implemented and checked against exhaustive computation on $903$ stopping
games, reproducing the exact value vector every time, and a second
implementation written independently from the statement reproduced it on
$4000$ random stopping games with three to twelve non-sinks, both players
present and sinks as successors, again without a mismatch. The theorem is a
rediscovery and is attributed as one: the decoder is the reconstruction
half of Gimbert and Horn's algorithm for games with few random vertices,
which enumerates the orderings of the random vertices and rebuilds the
value vector from each; what is added here is only the statement that the
order is a certificate and the count of comparisons it costs.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The consistent order is unique, and only the read letters count]\label{rem:order-unique}
Three additions from the round-$17$ order-lattice route, each a few lines
from \Cref{thm:order-determines,def:residue,lem:trapchar,thm:contraction}.
Call a total preorder $\preceq$ on $A:=\Vavg\cup\{t_0,t_1\}$
\emph{consistent} if some pair $(\sigma,\tau)$ selected from it as in the
proof above --- equivalently by the backward induction of \Cref{def:residue}
run with the ranks of $\preceq$ as ordinal payoffs --- has $v_{\sigma,\tau}$
inducing $\preceq$ again on $A$.
(i) \emph{On a stopping game the true preorder is the only consistent one.}
If $x:=v_{\sigma,\tau}$ induces $\preceq$, a strictly increasing map carries
the ranks to the values $x|_A$ and commutes with $\max$ and $\min$, so the
residue $D$ of \Cref{def:residue} run with the payoffs $x|_A$ has
$\sigma,\tau$ attaining its extrema; along a topological order of the
acyclic controlled subgraph (\Cref{lem:trapchar}) one gets
$x(v)=x(\text{chosen successor})=D(v)$, hence $x(v)=\max_ix(v^{(i)})$ at
$\Vmax$ and $\min_i$ at $\Vmin$, so $Tx=x$ and $x=w^\ast$
(\Cref{thm:contraction}). The certificate of \Cref{thm:order-determines}
is therefore unique --- \textsc{SSG-Value} lies in $\mathrm{UP}\cap\mathrm{coUP}$
with a witness of $O(a\log a)$ bits, a membership known before this
document (from memory, unchecked), the witness here being the order ---
which changes nothing
algorithmically (the remark after \Cref{prop:bracket}); iterating
``decode, then re-sort'' from an arbitrary preorder is not an algorithm
either (\Cref{rem:cyclic-antipodal}).
(ii) The decoder reads $\preceq$ only on
$B:=\{t_0,t_1\}\cup\{p\in\Vavg:\ p\text{ a successor of a controlled vertex}\}$,
every play of the residue stopping at such a $p$, so the witness has
$O(b\log b)$ bits with $b:=|B|\le\min(a+2,\,2|C|+2)$; but $b$ is not a
new parameter: a directed cycle through a controlled vertex leaves $C$
into $B$, so $\mu_k\le b-2$ and bounded $b$ lies inside \Cref{thm:modulator}.
(iii) Stopping is necessary: on the non-stopping game $v\in\Vmax$,
$v\to(v,p)$, $p\to(t_1,q)$, $q\to(v,t_0)$, with
$\val=(\tfrac23,\tfrac23,\tfrac13)$ (\Cref{thm:lfp-general}), both
$t_0\prec q\prec p\prec t_1$ and $t_0\sim q\prec p\prec t_1$ are consistent
under the attractor decoder of the proof, the second through the
selection $\sigma(v)=v$, whose trap gives $v_\sigma=(0,\tfrac12,0)$ by
\Cref{thm:eval-stopfree}. Checked exhaustively over every total preorder
on the letters of $76$ random stopping games with up to four controlled
vertices and every tie-breaking of the decode
(\texttt{scripts/round17-verify/ol\_verify.py}).
\end{remark}

The atomic step of the selection problem is itself exactly as hard.


\Cref{thm:order-determines} makes the certificate small, and it invites a
divide-and-conquer: if one could reduce an instance with $a$ average
vertices to polynomially many with at most $a/2$, then
\Cref{thm:few-avg} and recursion would give a quasi-polynomial
$N^{O(\log a)}$ algorithm. Both mechanisms available for such a reduction
are unavailable, and for reasons already proved.

\begin{proposition}[Neither substitution nor order-splitting halves
$a$]\label{prop:no-halving}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item If a sub-game with $k$ average vertices has value $p$ at some
      vertex, then $p$ has denominator dividing an integer at most $2^{k}$.
      Hence realising a constant whose denominator is $2^{a}$ requires at
      least $a$ fresh average vertices, and denominators $2^{a}$ occur:
      on the ladder $L_n$ of \Cref{def:ladder} the vertex $w_1$ has
      denominator exactly $2^{n}$. So, in the worst case --- when that value has denominator
      $2^{a}$ and the gadget is built from fresh average vertices, hence
      is disjoint from the $a-1$ that remain --- replacing an average
      vertex by a gadget realising its now-known value takes $a$ to at
      least $2a-1$.
\item Deciding $w^\ast(x)\ge w^\ast(y)$ for two \emph{average} vertices
      $x,y$ of a stopping SSG is polynomial-time equivalent to
      \Cref{prob:main}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(a) is \Cref{lem:denominator-sharp} applied to the sub-game, together
with the computation of $\val_{L_n}$ in \Cref{thm:ladder}; the sub-game
must be disjoint from the $a-1$ average vertices that remain, whence the
count.

(b) One direction is \Cref{thm:compare-equivalence}. For the other,
given a stopping $G$ and arbitrary vertices $p,q$, adjoin two fresh
average vertices $p'\to(p,p)$ and $q'\to(q,q)$. Then
$w^\ast(p')=w^\ast(p)$ and $w^\ast(q')=w^\ast(q)$ by
\Cref{lem:splice}(a) read backwards, and the enlarged game is stopping,
since a trap containing $p'$ must contain $p$ and conversely. So the
comparison of $p$ and $q$ is a comparison of two average vertices.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:no-halving}
The two clauses close the two doors. A reduction that \emph{learns} the
value of an average vertex and substitutes it pays for the substitution
in the very currency it is trying to save, by (a); and a reduction that
splits the average vertices by their \emph{order}, which
\Cref{thm:order-determines} says is all the information there is, must
perform a comparison of two average values, which by (b) is the whole
problem. This does not refute every conceivable halving --- a reduction
might produce sub-instances by some route that is neither --- but it does
say that the two mechanisms the certificate itself suggests are both
dead, and it explains why no quasi-polynomial bound appears anywhere in
this document.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The same question for $|C|$]\label{rem:no-halving-controlled}
\Cref{prop:no-halving} is about $a$. The problem is fixed-parameter tractable in
$|C|=|\Vmax|+|\Vmin|$ as well --- by \Cref{thm:determinacy} the value is
$\max_\sigma\min_\tau v_{\sigma,\tau}$ over the $2^{|C|}$ positional pairs, and
each $v_{\sigma,\tau}$ costs one application of \Cref{thm:eval-stopfree} --- so
the same halving question arises there, and a reduction of one instance to
polynomially many with at most $|C|/2$ controlled vertices would again give
$N^{O(\log N)}$. That door is shut too, but by \Cref{thm:fold} rather than by
\Cref{prop:no-halving}.

By \Cref{lem:transport-dim} a point of $Q(G)$ is the harmonic extension of its
restriction to $C$, so the game is equivalent to the fixed-point system
$y=\Phi(y)$ on $\mathbb{R}^{C}$ in which each coordinate $\Phi(y)_v$ is a
maximum or a minimum of two substochastic affine functions of $y$. The reduction
that structure suggests is to fix the coordinates on a subset $S\subseteq C$,
solve the residual system --- an instance with $|C|-|S|$ controlled vertices ---
and close the loop on $S$. Already $|S|=1$ defeats it. Let $P'_D$ be the game
$P_D$ of \Cref{thm:fold} with $u$ declared a Max vertex, its two out-edges
$u\to(t_0,t_1)$ unchanged. Both successors of $u$ are sinks, so $u$ lies in no
trap in the sense of \Cref{lem:trapchar} whatever its kind, and the iterated
deletion computing the largest trap runs identically in $P_D$ and in $P'_D$;
hence $P'_D$ is stopping. Freezing deletes $u$'s out-edges, so
$P'_D[u{:=}\theta]=P_D[u{:=}\theta]$ and \Cref{thm:fold} applies verbatim. Now
$P'_D$ has $|C|=2D+1$, freezing its controlled vertex $u$ leaves a game with
$2D$ controlled vertices, and the residual value $R_{u,F_D}$ is not affine in
$\theta$ but piecewise affine with exactly $2^{D}=2^{(|C|-1)/2}$ pieces. So the
outer problem on $S$ is not an instance of \Cref{prob:main} with $|S|$
controlled vertices, and a case analysis over the pieces is exponential in
$|C|$ from the very first frozen coordinate.

As in \Cref{rem:no-halving}, this refutes the mechanism that the structure
theory itself suggests, not every conceivable halving. The asymmetry between the
two parameters is worth recording: substituting a \emph{known} average value
costs $a$ fresh average vertices by \Cref{prop:no-halving}(a), whereas
substituting a known controlled decision is free --- it deletes an edge and
nothing else --- and what fails on the controlled side is instead that the
dependence on the substituted datum is exponentially many-branched.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Deciding wrongness is the whole problem]\label{cor:wrong-equivalence}
Call $v\in\Vmax$ \emph{wrong} at $\sigma$ if $\sigma(v)\notin A_v$, i.e.\
if $\sigma$ does not choose a $w^\ast$-maximising successor at $v$. The
problem \textsc{Wrong-Vertex} --- given a stopping SSG, a Max strategy
$\sigma$ and a vertex $v\in\Vmax$, decide whether $v$ is wrong at
$\sigma$ --- is polynomial-time equivalent to \textsc{SSG-Value}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Write $\{a,b\}$ for the two successors of $v$ with $\sigma(v)=a$. Then
$v$ is wrong at $\sigma$ exactly when $w^\ast(b)>w^\ast(a)$, so a single
\textsc{SSG-Compare} query decides it; by
\Cref{thm:compare-equivalence} this is a polynomial-time reduction to
\textsc{SSG-Value}.

Conversely, given a stopping SSG $H$ and vertices $a,b$, adjoin one fresh
Max vertex $v$ with $v\to\{a,b\}$. Nothing reaches $v$, so all values of
$H$ are unchanged and the enlarged game is still stopping. Let $\sigma$
be any Max strategy of the enlarged game with $\sigma(v)=a$. Then $v$ is
wrong at $\sigma$ if and only if $\val_H(b)>\val_H(a)$, i.e.\ if and only
if the answer to \textsc{SSG-Compare} on $(H,a,b)$ is negative. So
\textsc{SSG-Compare}, and hence \textsc{SSG-Value}, reduces to
\textsc{Wrong-Vertex}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
\Cref{thm:compare-equivalence} sharpens \Cref{cor:selection}. The
difficulty of \textsc{SSG-Value} is neither the length of improving paths
(linear, \Cref{thm:short-path}) nor the amount of information required
(exactly $n$ bits, one per Max vertex, and they need not be obtained
adaptively). It is that each individual bit is itself an instance of the
original problem. Any polynomial algorithm must therefore extract these
$n$ bits by some global mechanism rather than by resolving them one at a
time --- and no such mechanism is supplied by anything proved here.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Naming the top average vertex is the whole problem]\label{thm:top}
Let \textsc{Top} be the problem: given a stopping SSG with
$\Vavg\ne\emptyset$, output an average vertex of maximum value. Then
\textsc{Top} is polynomial-time equivalent to \Cref{prob:main}, by a
reduction making a single oracle call.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
One direction is trivial. For the other, by \Cref{prop:no-halving}(b) it
suffices to reduce the comparison of two vertices $p,q$ of a stopping $G$
to \textsc{Top}. Compute $\{\val=1\}$ and $\{\val=0\}$ exactly, by the
attractor sweeps of \Cref{def:simorder} run to their fixed points (the
sound under-approximations that definition also allows would not do
here), delete their vertices and redirect every
edge into them to $t_1$ and $t_0$; the remaining values are unchanged,
the game stays stopping, and every remaining non-sink value lies in
$(0,1)$. If $p$ or $q$ has been absorbed the
comparison is decided; otherwise let $a:=|\Vavg|$ of the reduced game
(if $a=0$ every value is $0$ or $1$), $r:=2a+2$ and $m:=r+2$. Adjoin a
reference chain $e_1\to(t_1,t_0)$, $e_{j+1}\to(t_1,e_j)$ of $r$ average
vertices, so $\val(e_j)=1-2^{-j}$, and two boost chains
$c^{(1)}_p\to(t_1,p)$, $c^{(j+1)}_p\to(t_1,c^{(j)}_p)$ and
$c^{(1)}_q\to(e_r,q)$, $c^{(j+1)}_q\to(t_1,c^{(j)}_q)$ of $m$ average
vertices each. Nothing new is a successor of anything old, so old values
are unchanged and the game is stopping; by induction
$\val(c^{(m)}_p)=1-2^{-m}(1-\val(p))$ and
$\val(c^{(m)}_q)=1-2^{-m}(1-\val(q)+2^{-r})$. Both exceed
$1-2^{-(r+1)}$, hence every $\val(e_j)\le1-2^{-r}$ and every old average
value, which is at most $1-2^{-a}$ by \Cref{lem:denominator-sharp}; within
each boost chain the values increase with $j$, so the maximum over
$\Vavg$ is attained at $c^{(m)}_p$ or at $c^{(m)}_q$, and at the former
iff $\val(p)>\val(q)-2^{-r}$. Two distinct old values differ by at least
$4^{-a}>2^{-r}$ and $\val(q)-\val(p)=2^{-r}$ is impossible, so the
maximiser is unique and names $c^{(m)}_p$ exactly when
$\val(p)\ge\val(q)$. The construction adds $r+2m=O(a)$ vertices.
(Reproduced here on $150$ random stopping games with interior values.)
\end{proof}

So \textsc{Top} joins \Cref{thm:compare-equivalence,thm:order-determines,%
thm:decide-one-bit,prop:no-halving,cor:wrong-equivalence,rem:alphabet-equivalence}
on the list of target-equivalent reformulations: naming the top average
vertex buys the whole game and costs the whole game, and the peeling
recursion it suggests --- know the top letter, remove it, recurse --- is
blocked at its first step by \Cref{prop:no-halving}(a), since removing a
letter means substituting a known value, at the cost of $a$ fresh
average vertices. Note the contrast with the threshold: by
\Cref{lem:alphabet-cover} every letter in $(0,1)$ is the midpoint of two
others, so the largest of them is at least $\tfrac12$, and some vertex
has value at least $\tfrac12$ unless every value is $0$ or $1$, which
the attractor sweeps decide in linear time; the threshold plays no part
in the difficulty, the naming of a vertex does.

\subsection{What a certificate would look like}

It is worth recording exactly what object a polynomial-time algorithm
would have to produce, because the answer is short and shows that the
certificate system is not the difficulty.

\begin{definition}[Bracket]\label{def:bracket}
A \emph{bracket} for a stopping SSG is a pair $(x,y)\in\mathcal{X}^2$ with
$x\le Tx$ and $Ty\le y$. It \emph{decides} an ordered pair of vertices
$(p,q)$ if $x(p)>y(q)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Brackets are sound, complete, and as hard as the problem]\label{prop:bracket}
Let $G$ be a stopping SSG.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item \emph{(Soundness)} If a bracket decides $(p,q)$ then $w^\ast(p)>w^\ast(q)$.
\item \emph{(Completeness)} $(w^\ast,w^\ast)$ is a bracket, and it decides
  $(p,q)$ whenever $w^\ast(p)>w^\ast(q)$.
\item Deciding whether a given pair $(x,y)$ is a bracket, and whether it
  decides a given $(p,q)$, takes $O(N)$ arithmetic operations.
\item There is a polynomial-time algorithm which, for every stopping SSG
  and every $v\in\Vmax$ whose two successors have distinct values, outputs
  a bracket deciding them in the correct order, \emph{if and only if}
  \textsc{SSG-Value} is decidable in polynomial time.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(a) By \Cref{cor:comparison}, $x\le Tx$ gives $x\le w^\ast$ and $Ty\le y$
gives $w^\ast\le y$. Hence $w^\ast(p)\ge x(p)>y(q)\ge w^\ast(q)$.
(b) $Tw^\ast=w^\ast$, so $(w^\ast,w^\ast)$ is a bracket, and it decides
exactly the true strict comparisons. (c) Both tests are $N$ evaluations of
$T$ at one coordinate each.

(d) ($\Rightarrow$) Running the algorithm at each $v\in\Vmax$ yields, by
(a), the correct sign of $w^\ast(v^{(0)})-w^\ast(v^{(1)})$ whenever the two
differ, and when it fails to produce a bracket the two values are equal
and either choice is greedy. This is exactly the list of $n$ comparison
bits of \Cref{thm:compare-equivalence}, from which an optimal $\sigma$ and
then $\val$ follow in polynomial time. ($\Leftarrow$) If
\textsc{SSG-Value} is in $\mathrm{P}$ then, by the non-adaptive part of
\Cref{thm:compare-equivalence}, $w^\ast$ is computable in polynomial time,
and $(w^\ast,w^\ast)$ is the required bracket by (b).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
So the certificate system is complete and its verification is trivial;
what is missing is a way to \emph{find} a bracket other than by computing
$w^\ast$ itself. \Cref{prop:bracket}(d) makes precise that this is not a
gap that a cleverer certificate format could close --- any sound complete
format has the same property, since by (b) the value vector is always a
witness and by (a) no witness can be wrong. Soundness was also checked
numerically: over $713$ randomly generated brackets on stopping games,
the $3506$ comparisons they decided were correct without exception.
\end{remark}

\section{A subexponential upper bound}\label{sec:randomfacet}

This section contains the strongest positive algorithmic result we can
prove: a Las~Vegas algorithm deciding \Cref{prob:main} in expected time
$e^{2\sqrt n}\,\mathrm{poly}(N)$, where $n=|\Vmax|$. It is subexponential
and not polynomial, and it is not ours: the algorithm is Ludwig's
random-facet algorithm for simple stochastic games, the specialisation to
this problem of the random-facet scheme of Kalai and of Matou\v{s}ek,
Sharir and Welzl, whose recurrence \Cref{def:frec} and its $e^{2\sqrt m}$
solution \Cref{lem:fbound} are theirs; the bound was later extended by
Bj\"orklund and Vorobyov. What is done here is to reprove it from the
foundations of this document, without genericity or perturbation
assumptions, stating and proving the subgame apparatus that the analysis
silently needs; nothing in the section is new.

Throughout this section $G$ is a \emph{stopping} SSG and Max strategies
are identified with $\{0,1\}^{\Vmax}$. Recall $\Phi(x)=\sum_{u\in V}x(u)$
and write $\Phi(\pi):=\Phi(\val_\pi)$.

\subsection{Subcubes, subgames and dead coordinates}

\begin{definition}\label{def:subcube}
Let $F\subseteq\Vmax$ and let $\rho:F\to\{0,1\}$ fix a bit at each $v\in F$
(subcubes are taken at the level of bits throughout, as in
\Cref{sec:structure}; when the two successors of $v$ coincide the two bits
are distinct strategies with the same behaviour, and nothing below
distinguishes them). The \emph{subcube} $C=C(\rho)$ is the set of Max
strategies extending $\rho$; its \emph{free} coordinates are
$\mathrm{free}(C)=\Vmax\setminus F$ and $n_C:=|\mathrm{free}(C)|$. The
\emph{subgame} $G_C$ is $G$ with \emph{both} out-edges of each $v\in F$
redirected to $\rho(v)$. For a free $w$ and a successor $b$ of $w$,
$C_{w,b}$ denotes the facet of $C$ additionally fixing $w$ to $b$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Subgame basics]\label{lem:subgame}
Let $C$ be a subcube of a stopping SSG $G$. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item $G_C$ is stopping, and the map sending a Max strategy of $G_C$ to its
  restriction-and-completion in $C$ is value-preserving: for every Max
  strategy $\pi'$ of $G_C$ there is $\pi\in C$ inducing the same Markov
  chains, and conversely. (Formally $G_C$ still has $2^{|\Vmax|}$ Max
  strategies, but the choices at the fixed vertices are inert because both
  their out-edges coincide;)
\item for $\pi\in C$ the vector $\val_\pi$ is the same whether computed
  in $G$ or in $G_C$;
\item writing $\opt(C)$ for the value vector of $G_C$, we have
  $\opt(C)\ge\val_\pi$ pointwise for every $\pi\in C$, with equality for
  at least one $\pi\in C$;
\item $C'\subseteq C$ implies $\opt(C')\le\opt(C)$ pointwise;
\item $\pi\in C$ has no strictly switchable coordinate in
  $\mathrm{free}(C)$ if and only if $\val_\pi=\opt(C)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) Every positional pair of $G_C$ arises from a positional pair of $G$
(extend $\rho$ arbitrarily) inducing the same Markov chain; since $G$ is
stopping, so is $G_C$. The correspondence of strategies is immediate from
\Cref{def:subcube}. (b) For $\pi\in C$ the chains $M_{\pi,\tau}$ in $G$
and in $G_C$ coincide for every $\tau$, so the pointwise minima over
$\tau$ agree. (c) Apply \Cref{thm:stopping-determinacy} to $G_C$ and use
(b). (d) $G_{C'}$ has fewer Max strategies, all of them strategies of
$G_C$, so the maximum in (c) is over a subset. (e) Apply
\Cref{lem:local-global} to $G_C$, using (b).
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Facet potentials and dead coordinates]\label{def:dead}
For a subcube $C$, a free coordinate $w$ and a successor $b$ of $w$ put
$s(C;w,b):=\Phi\bigl(\opt(C_{w,b})\bigr)$. Given also $\sigma\in C$, call
a free coordinate $z$ \emph{dead} for $(C,\sigma)$ if
$s(C;z,\bar\sigma(z))<\Phi(\sigma)$, and let $\mathrm{alive}(C,\sigma)$
be the number of free coordinates that are not dead.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Facet exclusion]\label{lem:facet-exclusion}
Let $\pi\in C$ and let $w$ be free with successor $b$. If
$\Phi(\pi)>s(C;w,b)$ then $\pi(w)\ne b$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $\pi(w)=b$ then $\pi\in C_{w,b}$, so $\val_\pi\le\opt(C_{w,b})$ by
\Cref{lem:subgame}(c) and hence $\Phi(\pi)\le s(C;w,b)$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The algorithm and its correctness}

\begin{definition}[Random facet]\label{def:rf}
$\mathrm{RF}(C,\sigma)$, for a subcube $C$ of a stopping SSG and
$\sigma\in C$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2.2em]
\item if $n_C=0$, return $(\sigma,\val_\sigma)$;
\item pick $v\in\mathrm{free}(C)$ uniformly at random;
\item $(\sigma^{\!*},\val_{\sigma^{\!*}}):=\mathrm{RF}\bigl(C_{v,\sigma(v)},\sigma\bigr)$;
\item if $v$ is not strictly switchable at $\sigma^{\!*}$ --- the test
  being made in $G$ itself, equivalently in $G_C$, where $v$ is still
  free, and not in $G_{C_{v,\sigma(v)}}$, where its two out-edges have
  been identified --- return
  $(\sigma^{\!*},\val_{\sigma^{\!*}})$;
\item otherwise set $\sigma^{\!**}:=\sigma^{\!*}[v]$ and return
  $\mathrm{RF}\bigl(C_{v,\bar\sigma(v)},\sigma^{\!**}\bigr)$.
\end{enumerate}
A \emph{switch} is an execution of step~5; $S(C,\sigma)$ denotes their
total number.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Correctness]\label{thm:rf-correct}
$\mathrm{RF}(C,\sigma)$ terminates and returns a strategy $\pi\in C$ with
$\val_\pi=\opt(C)$. Moreover every strategy it holds has value vector
pointwise at least that of its predecessor.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Induction on $n_C$. If $n_C=0$ then $C=\{\sigma\}$ and
$\val_\sigma=\opt(C)$ by \Cref{lem:subgame}(c). Let $n_C\ge1$ and pick
$v$. By induction $\sigma^{\!*}$ satisfies
$\val_{\sigma^{\!*}}=\opt(C_{v,\sigma(v)})$, and
$\sigma^{\!*}(v)=\sigma(v)$.

If step~4 applies, then $\sigma^{\!*}$ has no strictly switchable
coordinate among $\mathrm{free}(C_{v,\sigma(v)})$, by
\Cref{lem:subgame}(e) applied to $C_{v,\sigma(v)}$, and $v$ is not
strictly switchable either; so $\sigma^{\!*}$ has no strictly switchable
coordinate in $\mathrm{free}(C)$, and \Cref{lem:subgame}(e) applied to
$C$ gives $\val_{\sigma^{\!*}}=\opt(C)$.

Otherwise, by \Cref{lem:switch}, $\val_{\sigma^{\!**}}\ge\val_{\sigma^{\!*}}$
pointwise with strict inequality at $v$, and
$\sigma^{\!**}\in C_{v,\bar\sigma(v)}$. By induction the final call
returns $\pi$ with $\val_\pi=\opt(C_{v,\bar\sigma(v)})\ge\val_{\sigma^{\!**}}$.
Hence $\opt(C_{v,\bar\sigma(v)})\ge\val_{\sigma^{\!*}}=\opt(C_{v,\sigma(v)})$
pointwise and the inequality is strict at $v$. By
\Cref{lem:subgame}(c) some $\pi^{\!*}\in C$ attains $\opt(C)$, and
$\pi^{\!*}$ lies in one of the two facets, so $\opt(C)$ equals
$\opt(C_{v,\sigma(v)})$ or $\opt(C_{v,\bar\sigma(v)})$; since both are
$\le\opt(C)$ by \Cref{lem:subgame}(d) and the second strictly dominates
the first, $\opt(C)=\opt(C_{v,\bar\sigma(v)})=\val_\pi$.

The recursion terminates because each call has exactly one free
coordinate fewer than its parent, so the recursion tree has depth at most
$n_C$; that every switch strictly increases $\Phi$ (\Cref{lem:switch})
bounds the number of switches in addition, but is not what terminates the
recursion. Monotonicity of the held values is \Cref{lem:switch} together
with the inductive statement.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The expected number of switches}

\begin{definition}\label{def:frec}
Let $f(0):=0$ and, for $m\ge1$,
$f(m):=f(m-1)+1+\frac1m\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}f(j)$.
\end{definition}

Since $f\ge0$, the recursion gives $f(m)-f(m-1)\ge1>0$, so $f$ is
strictly increasing.

\begin{theorem}[Switch bound]\label{thm:rf-bound}
For every stopping SSG, every subcube $C$ and every $\sigma\in C$,
\[
  \mathbb{E}\bigl[S(C,\sigma)\bigr]\;\le\;f\bigl(\mathrm{alive}(C,\sigma)\bigr)\;\le\;f(n_C).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Strong induction on $n_C$; note that both recursive calls have exactly
$n_C-1$ free coordinates, so the induction is well founded. (The
induction cannot be run on $\mathrm{alive}$, which need not decrease.)
For $n_C=0$ there are no switches and $f(0)=0$.

Write $m:=\mathrm{alive}(C,\sigma)$, $k:=n_C-m$ for the number of dead
coordinates, and
\[
  D\;:=\;\bigl\{w\in\mathrm{free}(C)\;:\;\opt(C_{w,\sigma(w)})\ne\opt(C)\bigr\},
  \qquad d:=|D| .
\]
By \Cref{lem:subgame}(d), $\opt(C_{w,\sigma(w)})\le\opt(C)$ always, so
membership of $D$ means strict pointwise domination.

\smallskip\noindent\emph{Step 1: dead $\Rightarrow$ not in $D$.}
Let $z$ be dead and let $\pi\in C$ attain $\opt(C)$
(\Cref{lem:subgame}(c)). Then $\Phi(\pi)=\Phi(\opt(C))\ge\Phi(\sigma)$.
If $\pi(z)=\bar\sigma(z)$ then \Cref{lem:facet-exclusion} would need
$\Phi(\pi)\le s(C;z,\bar\sigma(z))<\Phi(\sigma)\le\Phi(\pi)$, absurd.
Hence $\pi(z)=\sigma(z)$, so $\pi\in C_{z,\sigma(z)}$ and
$\opt(C_{z,\sigma(z)})\ge\val_\pi=\opt(C)$; with
\Cref{lem:subgame}(d) this forces equality, i.e.\ $z\notin D$. In
particular $D$ consists of alive coordinates and $d\le m$.

\smallskip\noindent\emph{Step 2: a switch occurs if and only if $v\in D$.}
By \Cref{thm:rf-correct}, $\val_{\sigma^{\!*}}=\opt(C_{v,\sigma(v)})$ and
$\sigma^{\!*}(v)=\sigma(v)$. If $v\notin D$ then
$\val_{\sigma^{\!*}}=\opt(C)$, which satisfies the Shapley equation of
$G_C$ at the free Max vertex $v$; hence
$\val_{\sigma^{\!*}}(\bar\sigma(v))\le\val_{\sigma^{\!*}}(v)=\val_{\sigma^{\!*}}(\sigma^{\!*}(v))$
and $v$ is not strictly switchable. If $v\in D$ and $v$ were not strictly
switchable, then as in the proof of \Cref{thm:rf-correct} we would get
$\val_{\sigma^{\!*}}=\opt(C)$, contradicting
$\val_{\sigma^{\!*}}=\opt(C_{v,\sigma(v)})\ne\opt(C)$.

\smallskip\noindent\emph{Step 3: the first recursive call.}
Let $z\ne v$ be dead for $(C,\sigma)$. Since
$C_{v,\sigma(v)}\cap C_{z,\bar\sigma(z)}\subseteq C_{z,\bar\sigma(z)}$,
\Cref{lem:subgame}(d) gives
$s(C_{v,\sigma(v)};z,\bar\sigma(z))\le s(C;z,\bar\sigma(z))<\Phi(\sigma)$,
so $z$ is dead for $(C_{v,\sigma(v)},\sigma)$. Hence the first call has
alive count at most $m-1$ if $v$ is alive, and at most $m$ if $v$ is
dead.

\smallskip\noindent\emph{Step 4: the second recursive call.}
Suppose $v\in D$ is picked and the switch occurs; by Step~1, $v$ is
alive. Since $\sigma\in C_{v,\sigma(v)}$ we have
$\val_\sigma\le\opt(C_{v,\sigma(v)})=\val_{\sigma^{\!*}}$, so
$\Phi(\sigma^{\!*})\ge\Phi(\sigma)$, and by \Cref{lem:switch}
\[
  \Phi(\sigma^{\!**})\;>\;\Phi(\sigma^{\!*})\;=\;s(C;v,\sigma(v))
  \quad\text{and}\quad \Phi(\sigma^{\!**})>\Phi(\sigma).
\]
\emph{(i) The $k$ dead coordinates stay dead.} Let $z\ne v$ be dead for
$(C,\sigma)$. If $\sigma^{\!**}(z)=\bar\sigma(z)$ then
\Cref{lem:facet-exclusion} gives
$\Phi(\sigma^{\!**})\le s(C;z,\bar\sigma(z))<\Phi(\sigma)<\Phi(\sigma^{\!**})$,
absurd; so $\sigma^{\!**}(z)=\sigma(z)$ and therefore
$\overline{\sigma^{\!**}}(z)=\bar\sigma(z)$. Consequently
\[
  s\bigl(C_{v,\bar\sigma(v)};z,\overline{\sigma^{\!**}}(z)\bigr)
  \;=\;s\bigl(C_{v,\bar\sigma(v)};z,\bar\sigma(z)\bigr)
  \;\le\;s(C;z,\bar\sigma(z))\;<\;\Phi(\sigma)\;<\;\Phi(\sigma^{\!**}),
\]
so $z$ is dead for $(C_{v,\bar\sigma(v)},\sigma^{\!**})$.

\emph{(ii) $r(v)-1$ further coordinates become dead.} For $w\in D$ put
$r(w):=\bigl|\{w'\in D: s(C;w',\sigma(w'))\le s(C;w,\sigma(w))\}\bigr|\ge1$.
Let $w\in D$, $w\ne v$, with $s(C;w,\sigma(w))\le s(C;v,\sigma(v))$;
there are $r(v)-1$ such $w$, all alive by Step~1 and all free in
$C_{v,\bar\sigma(v)}$. Then
$\Phi(\sigma^{\!**})>s(C;v,\sigma(v))\ge s(C;w,\sigma(w))$, so
\Cref{lem:facet-exclusion} gives $\sigma^{\!**}(w)\ne\sigma(w)$, i.e.\
$\overline{\sigma^{\!**}}(w)=\sigma(w)$, and hence
\[
  s\bigl(C_{v,\bar\sigma(v)};w,\overline{\sigma^{\!**}}(w)\bigr)
  \;=\;s\bigl(C_{v,\bar\sigma(v)};w,\sigma(w)\bigr)
  \;\le\;s(C;w,\sigma(w))\;<\;\Phi(\sigma^{\!**}),
\]
so $w$ too is dead for $(C_{v,\bar\sigma(v)},\sigma^{\!**})$. The
coordinates in (i) and (ii) are distinct, so the second call has at least
$k+r(v)-1$ dead coordinates among its $n_C-1$ free ones, i.e.\ alive
count at most $(n_C-1)-(k+r(v)-1)=m-r(v)$.

\smallskip\noindent\emph{Step 5: assembling.}
Condition on the uniformly chosen $v$. The bounds of Steps~3 and~4 hold
\emph{deterministically} on the corresponding events, and the randomness
of each recursive call is independent of the choice of $v$, so the
induction hypothesis may be applied inside each branch and the
conditional expectations combined by the tower rule. Using Step~2 for
which branches incur a switch,
\[
  \mathbb{E}[S]\;\le\;\frac1{n_C}\Bigl[\,k\,f(m)\;+\;(m-d)f(m-1)\;+\;\sum_{v\in D}\bigl(f(m-1)+1+f(m-r(v))\bigr)\Bigr].
\]
Ordering $D$ by increasing $s(C;\cdot,\sigma(\cdot))$, its $j$-th element
has $r\ge j$; as $f$ is nondecreasing,
$\sum_{v\in D}f(m-r(v))\le\sum_{j=1}^{d}f(m-j)$. Since $f\ge0$ and
$d\le m$,
\[
  d+\sum_{j=1}^{d}f(m-j)\;\le\;m+\sum_{j=1}^{m}f(m-j)\;=\;m+\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}f(i).
\]
Therefore
\[
  \mathbb{E}[S]\;\le\;\frac1{n_C}\Bigl[k\,f(m)+m\Bigl(f(m-1)+1+\frac1m\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}f(i)\Bigr)\Bigr]
  \;=\;\frac{k\,f(m)+m\,f(m)}{n_C}\;=\;f(m),
\]
by \Cref{def:frec}. If $m=0$ then $D=\emptyset$ by Step~1, no switch ever
occurs, and $\mathbb{E}[S]=0=f(0)$; the term $m\,f(m-1)$ has coefficient
$0$ and the undefined value $f(-1)$ never arises. Finally
$f(m)\le f(n_C)$ because $m\le n_C$ and $f$ is nondecreasing.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:fbound}
$f(m)\le e^{2\sqrt m}$ for every $m\ge0$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $m\le8$ this is checked directly: the exact values are
$f(0..8)=0,\,1,\,\tfrac52,\,\tfrac{14}3,\,\tfrac{185}{24},\,\tfrac{713}{60},\,\tfrac{12607}{720},\,\tfrac{62941}{2520},\,\tfrac{1401409}{40320}$,
the largest being $f(8)\approx34.76$ against $e^{2\sqrt8}\approx286.2$.

Let $m\ge9$ and assume the bound for all smaller arguments. Put
$t:=1/\sqrt m\le\tfrac13$. Since $x\mapsto e^{2\sqrt x}$ is increasing,
$\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}e^{2\sqrt j}\le\int_0^{m}e^{2\sqrt x}\,dx=(\sqrt m-\tfrac12)e^{2\sqrt m}+\tfrac12$,
the antiderivative being $(\sqrt x-\tfrac12)e^{2\sqrt x}$. Also
$\sqrt m-\sqrt{m-1}=1/(\sqrt m+\sqrt{m-1})\ge 1/(2\sqrt m)=t/2$, so
$e^{2\sqrt{m-1}}\le e^{2\sqrt m}e^{-t}$. Hence
\[
  f(m)\;\le\;e^{2\sqrt m}e^{-t}+1+\frac1m\Bigl[(\sqrt m-\tfrac12)e^{2\sqrt m}+\tfrac12\Bigr]
  \;=\;e^{2\sqrt m}\bigl[e^{-t}+t-\tfrac{t^{2}}2\bigr]+1+\tfrac{t^{2}}2 .
\]
For $0<t\le1$ the alternating series with decreasing terms gives
$e^{-t}\le1-t+\tfrac{t^2}2-\tfrac{t^3}6+\tfrac{t^4}{24}$, so the bracket
is at most $1-\tfrac{t^3}6+\tfrac{t^4}{24}\le1-\tfrac{t^3}8$. It
therefore suffices that $e^{2\sqrt m}\tfrac{t^3}8\ge1+\tfrac{t^2}2$.
With $u:=\sqrt m\ge3$ this reads $e^{2u}\ge8u^{3}\bigl(1+\tfrac1{2u^{2}}\bigr)$,
which is implied by $e^{2u}\ge12u^{3}$ since
$1+\tfrac1{2u^2}\le\tfrac32$ for $u\ge1$. Finally
$h(u):=2u-\ln12-3\ln u$ satisfies $h(3)=6-\ln12-3\ln3\approx0.219>0$ and
$h'(u)=2-3/u>0$ for $u>\tfrac32$, so $h\ge0$ for all $u\ge3$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Subexponential decision procedure]\label{thm:subexp}
There is a Las~Vegas algorithm that decides \Cref{prob:main} --- always
correctly --- in expected time $e^{2\sqrt n}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(N)$, where
$n=|\Vmax|$ and $N=|V|$ of the input game.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Given $G$ and $v_0$, build the stopping game $G_m$ of
\Cref{thm:stopping-transform}; it has $O(N(a+\log N))$ vertices and the
same set $\Vmax$, hence the same $n$. Run $\mathrm{RF}$ on the full cube
from an arbitrary initial strategy. By \Cref{thm:rf-correct} it returns a
strategy $\pi$ with $\val_\pi=\val_{G_m}$; comparing $\val_\pi(v_0)$
against the threshold $\tfrac12-\tfrac1{4D}$ of
\Cref{thm:stopping-transform} decides $\val_G(v_0)\ge\tfrac12$, so the
output is always correct.

For the running time: by \Cref{thm:rf-bound,lem:fbound} the expected
number of switches is at most $f(n)\le e^{2\sqrt n}$. The recursion tree
has at most $(S+1)(n+1)$ nodes, since between two consecutive switches
the recursion descends a chain of at most $n+1$ calls; by linearity of
expectation the expected number of nodes is $O\bigl(e^{2\sqrt n}n\bigr)$.
Each node performs one evaluation of $\val_\sigma$, which is the unique
optimum of the linear program in the proof of
\Cref{thm:compare-equivalence} --- correct because $G_m$ is stopping, by
\Cref{cor:comparison} --- together with $O(n)$ comparisons of rationals
of polynomial bit-size (\Cref{lem:denominator}). Hence the expected total
time is $e^{2\sqrt n}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(N)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The algorithm was implemented and run in exact rational arithmetic on
stopping games; it returned the exact optimum in all $692$ runs, and the
number of switches never exceeded $f(\cdot)$. Independently, the bound of
\Cref{thm:rf-bound} was tested on roughly $13{,}000$ subcube/strategy
pairs drawn from $185$ stopping games with no violation, and each of the
five steps of its proof was checked separately (over $50{,}000$
individual checks). \Cref{lem:fbound} was verified numerically far beyond
the range needed, the ratio $f(m)/e^{2\sqrt m}$ peaking at $0.148$ at
$m=2$ and decreasing thereafter.

We stress what \Cref{thm:subexp} is not. The bound $e^{2\sqrt n}$ is
subexponential but superpolynomial, so this does not touch
\Cref{prob:main}. Nor do we claim the analysis is tight: the recurrence
bound is attained at $\mathrm{alive}=1$, where $\mathbb{E}[S]=f(1)=1$, but
on the instances we could generate the ratio $\mathbb{E}[S]/f(m)$ falls
off steadily with $m$ --- the best observed values were
$0.80,0.71,0.56,0.45$ at $m=2,3,4,5$ --- so the true worst-case behaviour
of the algorithm may well be better than $f(n)$. What we assert is only
that $f(n)$ is a proved upper bound.
\end{remark}

\section{The remaining gap}\label{sec:gap}

Everything above is proved. The following statement is not, and it is
exactly what a polynomial-time algorithm along these lines requires.

\begin{definition}[Switching rule]\label{def:rule}
A \emph{switching rule} is a polynomial-time computable map $R$ that,
given a stopping SSG $G$, the history $\sigma_0,\dots,\sigma_t$ of
strategies produced so far and the value vector $\val_{\sigma_t}$,
returns a nonempty subset $R(\cdot)\subseteq S_{\sigma_t}$ whenever
$S_{\sigma_t}\ne\emptyset$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[The missing statement]\label{def:missing}
\textsc{(Poly-Rule)} \emph{There exist a switching rule $R$ and a
polynomial $p$ such that for every stopping SSG $G$ and every initial
strategy $\sigma_0$, the iteration $\sigma_{t+1}:=\sigma_t[R(\cdot)]$
reaches a strategy with $S_\sigma=\emptyset$ within $p(|V(G)|)$ steps.}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[The gap is exactly the problem]\label{thm:gap-equivalence}
\textsc{(Poly-Rule)} holds if and only if \textsc{SSG-Value} is decidable
in polynomial time.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
($\Rightarrow$) Given $G$ and $v_0$, build $G'$ by
\Cref{thm:stopping-transform} in polynomial time --- it is the damped
game $G_m$ of \Cref{def:damping}, renamed here so as not to clash with
the value-iteration family $G_m$ of \Cref{thm:vi-lower}; $G'$ is stopping
and of size $O(N(a+\log N))$. Run the iteration of \Cref{def:missing}
from an arbitrary $\sigma_0$. Each step requires evaluating
$\val_{\sigma_t}$, which is the unique optimum of the linear program
displayed in the proof of \Cref{thm:compare-equivalence}; that program's
feasible set is contained in $\{x\in\mathcal{X}:x\le T_{\sigma_t}x\}$ and
contains $\val_{\sigma_t}$, which is therefore its optimum by
\Cref{cor:comparison}, precisely because $G'$ is stopping. (Policy
iteration for Min would be sound here too, the game being stopping ---
\Cref{ex:pi-fails} breaks it only without stopping --- but it is not
known to run in polynomial time, and the programme is.) Linear programs
of polynomial bit-size are solvable in polynomial time. Then
$S_{\sigma_t}$ is computed by $|\Vmax|$ comparisons. By hypothesis the
iteration halts within $p(|V(G')|)$ steps at a strategy with
$S_\sigma=\emptyset$, which by \Cref{lem:local-global} is optimal, so
$\val_{\sigma}=\val_{G'}$. Comparing $\val_{G'}(v_0)$ with the explicit
threshold $\tfrac12-\tfrac1{4D}$ of \Cref{thm:stopping-transform} decides
$\val_G(v_0)\ge\tfrac12$. All steps are polynomial.

($\Leftarrow$) Suppose \textsc{SSG-Value} is decidable in polynomial time.
Let $G$ be a stopping SSG. By \Cref{thm:compare-equivalence},
\textsc{SSG-Compare} is then also decidable in polynomial time, so for
each $v\in\Vmax$ we may decide in polynomial time whether
$\val(v^{(0)})\ge\val(v^{(1)})$ and thereby compute, in $n$ queries, a
strategy $\sigma^\ast$ that is greedy with respect to $w^\ast$; by
\Cref{thm:opt-subcube}, $\sigma^\ast\in\Opt$. Define the switching rule
$R$ to be: compute $\sigma^\ast$ as above (this depends only on $G$, not
on the history) and return $\{v\}$ for the least $v\in S_{\sigma_t}$ with
$\sigma_t(v)\ne\sigma^\ast(v)$. The rule is polynomial-time computable,
and such a $v$ exists whenever $S_{\sigma_t}\ne\emptyset$ by
\Cref{thm:short-path}. Each application strictly decreases
$|\{v:\sigma_t(v)\ne\sigma^\ast(v)\}|$, so after at most $n$ iterations
the current strategy either equals $\sigma^\ast$ or has already reached
$S_\sigma=\emptyset$; in both cases the iteration halts within
$n\le|V(G)|$ steps. Thus $R$ witnesses \textsc{(Poly-Rule)} with
$p(x)=x$.
\end{proof}

\medskip

\Cref{def:missing} asks a rule to choose \emph{which improving switch to
perform}. A second, formally different shape has been at the centre of
several attempts: a rule that does not iterate strategies at all, but
merely resolves one vertex at a time, deciding which of its two
successors carries the larger optimal value and then fixing that vertex
for good. The brackets of \Cref{def:bracket} are of this shape, as is any
sound purely order-theoretic derivation of inequalities between vertex
values. \Cref{prop:bracket}(d) already records that producing a deciding
bracket at \emph{every} controlled vertex is equivalent to the problem;
what follows is the sharper statement, that one decided vertex per round,
by any mechanism whatever, is equally sufficient.

\begin{definition}[Decision rule]\label{def:decision-rule}
A \emph{decision rule} is a polynomial-time computable map $R$ that,
given a stopping SSG $G$, returns either the symbol $\bot$ or a pair
$(v,i)$ with $v\in\Vmax\cup\Vmin$ and $i\in\{0,1\}$. It is \emph{sound}
if $R(G)=(v,i)$ implies $w^\ast(v^{(i)})\ge w^\ast(v^{(1-i)})$, and
\emph{non-stalling} if $R(G)=\bot$ only when
$w^\ast(v^{(0)})=w^\ast(v^{(1)})$ for every $v\in\Vmax\cup\Vmin$ ---
that is, it abstains only when there is genuinely nothing left to decide.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[One bit per round is already the whole problem]\label{thm:decide-one-bit}
A sound non-stalling decision rule exists if and only if
\textsc{SSG-Value} is decidable in polynomial time.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
($\Leftarrow$) If \textsc{SSG-Value} is decidable in polynomial time then
so is \textsc{SSG-Compare} by \Cref{thm:compare-equivalence}. Scan the
controlled vertices in index order, and for each compare
$w^\ast(v^{(0)})$ with $w^\ast(v^{(1)})$; return $(v,i)$ for the first
$v$ whose two successors differ, taking $i$ with the larger value, and
$\bot$ if none differs. This rule is polynomial-time, sound and
non-stalling.

($\Rightarrow$) Let $R$ be sound and non-stalling. Given an arbitrary
SSG and start vertex $v_0$, first replace it by the stopping game $G_m$
of \Cref{thm:stopping-transform}; it suffices to compute $w^\ast$ of a
stopping game. Given a stopping $G$, run the following round.

Apply $R$. If $R(G)=(v,i)$, let
\[
  \pi(v):=\begin{cases} v^{(i)}, & v\in\Vmax,\\[2pt] v^{(1-i)}, & v\in\Vmin,\end{cases}
\]
--- Max is committed to the \emph{larger} successor, Min to the
\emph{smaller} one --- and let $G'$ be $G$ with $v$ turned into an
\emph{average} vertex both of whose out-edges lead to $\pi(v)$. The move
out of $v$ is thereby forced, and $v$ leaves the controlled set. We claim
$G'$ is stopping and $w^\ast_{G'}=w^\ast_G$.

A positional pair of $G'$ induces one of $G$ by choosing $\pi(v)$ at $v$,
and the two induce the same Markov chain, so absorption almost surely
under all pairs is inherited and $G'$ is stopping. For the values, write
$T'$ for the Shapley operator of $G'$. At every vertex other than $v$ the
two operators agree. At $v$ both successors are $\pi(v)$, so
$(T'w^\ast)(v)=\tfrac12w^\ast(\pi(v))+\tfrac12w^\ast(\pi(v))=w^\ast(\pi(v))$.
If $v\in\Vmax$ then soundness gives
$w^\ast(v^{(i)})=\max\{w^\ast(v^{(0)}),w^\ast(v^{(1)})\}$, so this equals
$(Tw^\ast)(v)=w^\ast(v)$; if $v\in\Vmin$ the same computation applies
with $\min$ and $\pi(v)=v^{(1-i)}$. Hence $T'w^\ast=w^\ast$, and since
$G'$ is stopping $T'$ has a unique fixed point by
\Cref{thm:contraction}, so $w^\ast_{G'}=w^\ast_G$ as claimed.

Each round therefore decreases $|\Vmax|+|\Vmin|$ by exactly one, so after
at most $|\Vmax|+|\Vmin|$ rounds the rule must return $\bot$. It is worth
saying why $v$ is retyped rather than merely having both its edges
redirected. Soundness is \emph{vacuous} at a vertex whose two successors
have equal optimal value, so a sound rule may legitimately return such a
vertex; redirecting its edges would then change nothing, and the rule
could return it again for ever. Retyping removes $v$ from the controlled
set whether or not it distinguished values, which is what makes the round
count unconditional.

When $R$ returns $\bot$, non-stalling says every controlled vertex $v$
has $w^\ast(v^{(0)})=w^\ast(v^{(1)})$. Choose \emph{any} positional pair
$(\sigma,\tau)$. At a Max vertex
$w^\ast(\sigma(v))=\max\{w^\ast(v^{(0)}),w^\ast(v^{(1)})\}$ because the
two agree, and likewise at Min, so $T_{\sigma,\tau}w^\ast=Tw^\ast=w^\ast$;
by \Cref{thm:contraction} again, $w^\ast=v_{\sigma,\tau}$, which is one
exact linear solve. Values are preserved along the whole run, so this is
$w^\ast$ of the original stopping game, and comparing it with the
threshold of \Cref{thm:stopping-transform} decides the instance. Each
round costs one call to $R$ plus polynomial bookkeeping, and there are at
most $|V|$ rounds.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The point of \Cref{thm:decide-one-bit} is where it places the burden.
It is not necessary to decide many vertices, nor to decide the
\emph{right} vertex, nor to make progress that any potential function can
see: extracting a single correct comparison per round, from a game one is
free to simplify between rounds, already gives a polynomial algorithm.
Consequently a sound rule of this shape can only fail by \emph{stalling}
--- abstaining on an instance in which some controlled vertex does
distinguish values --- and exhibiting one stalling instance refutes the
rule outright. This is what makes such rules falsifiable in a way that
\Cref{def:missing} is not: there the failure mode is a superpolynomial
\emph{number} of steps, which no single instance can exhibit.
\end{remark}

That single bit, however, is not available locally. Write $N_k(G,v)$ for
the radius-$k$ out-neighbourhood of $v$ in $G$, carrying vertex types,
the two sinks, and the order of each vertex's two out-edges.

\begin{proposition}[No bounded-radius decision rule is sound]\label{prop:locality}
Let $k\ge1$ and $L:=k+4$. There are stopping SSGs $G,G'$ on $2L+3$
vertices, all non-sink vertices average except a single Max vertex $v$,
with
\[
  N_k(G,v)=N_k(G',v)
  \quad\text{yet}\quad
  w^\ast_G(v^{(0)})-w^\ast_G(v^{(1)})=2^{-L}
  =-\bigl(w^\ast_{G'}(v^{(0)})-w^\ast_{G'}(v^{(1)})\bigr).
\]
Hence no sound decision rule (\Cref{def:decision-rule}) can compute its
answer at $v$ from $N_k(G,v)$ for any fixed $k$: the one comparison that
\Cref{thm:decide-one-bit} shows to be sufficient is not locally
checkable.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For a bit string $b=b_1\cdots b_L$ let $C(b)$ be the chain of average
vertices $c_1,\dots,c_L$ with $c_i\to\{z_i,c_{i+1}\}$ for $i<L$ and
$c_L\to\{z_L,t_0\}$, where $z_i:=t_1$ if $b_i=1$ and $z_i:=t_0$
otherwise. The chain is acyclic and every vertex reaches a sink, so the
Shapley operator has a unique fixed point, obtained by back-substitution:
$\val(c_i)=\tfrac12 b_i+\tfrac12\val(c_{i+1})$ with $\val(c_{L+1}):=0$,
whence $\val(c_1)=\sum_{i\le L}b_i2^{-i}$.

Fix any $\beta\in\{0,1\}^{k}$ and put
\[
  \alpha:=\beta\,1\,0^{L-k-1},\qquad
  \gamma:=\beta\,0\,1^{L-k-1}.
\]
Let $G$ consist of a Max vertex $v$ with $v^{(0)}$ the head of $C(\alpha)$
and $v^{(1)}$ the head of $C(\gamma)$, and let $G'$ be the same with
$\alpha$ and $\gamma$ exchanged. Both are stopping: every vertex reaches a
sink within $L+1$ steps under every pair, there being no choices at all
below $v$. Summing the geometric tail,
\[
  \val(C(\alpha))-\val(C(\gamma))
  =2^{-(k+1)}-\sum_{i=k+2}^{L}2^{-i}
  =2^{-(k+1)}-\bigl(2^{-(k+1)}-2^{-L}\bigr)=2^{-L},
\]
which is the displayed difference; exchanging the chains negates it.

Finally $N_k(G,v)=N_k(G',v)$. A vertex $c_i$ of either chain is at
distance $i$ from $v$ and its sink successor $z_i$ at distance $i+1$, so
$N_k$ exposes, of each chain, the vertices $c_1,\dots,c_k$ and the sinks
$z_1,\dots,z_{k-1}$ --- that is, exactly the first $k-1$ bits, and in any
case at most the first $k$. Since $\alpha$ and $\gamma$ agree on their
first $k$ bits, the two radius-$k$ neighbourhoods coincide, out-edge
order included, and the identity is an isomorphism between them. A rule
reading only $N_k(\cdot,v)$ therefore returns the same answer on $G$ and
on $G'$; abstaining is sound at both (\Cref{def:decision-rule}), so the
statement is about rules that decide at $v$, and one that answers
$(v,i)$ --- as a sound rule must at a vertex whose successors have
distinct values, if it does not stall --- is unsound on one of the two
games.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The two statements should be read together. \Cref{thm:decide-one-bit}
says the quantity of information needed is minimal --- one correct
comparison per round, on a game one may simplify in between.
\Cref{prop:locality} says that this single bit is nevertheless global:
the instances $G,G'$ differ only beyond distance $k$ from $v$, by a
margin $2^{-L}$ that is exponentially small in the size of the
perturbed region, and yet they reverse the answer at $v$. This is the
same phenomenon that defeats the potential arguments of
\Cref{sec:refutations}, in its sharpest form: the decision at a vertex is
a function of the entire instance, and no amount of local inspection,
however deep, replaces it.
\end{remark}

\medskip

The distinction between \Cref{def:missing} and \Cref{def:decision-rule} is
not cosmetic. A switching iteration measures progress only by changes to
$\sigma$, so a decision at a vertex that $\sigma$ already chooses
correctly is worthless to it; the same decision is genuine progress for a
decision rule, which retires that vertex from the controlled set. There
is an explicit stopping game separating the two.

\begin{proposition}[A switching stall that is not a decision stall]\label{prop:pdc-separation}
Let $G$ be the stopping SSG on non-sink vertices $0,\dots,28$ with
$t_0=29$, $t_1=30$, types
$(\max,\min,\min,\mathrm{avg},\max,\min,\mathrm{avg})$ followed by $22$
further average vertices, and out-edge pairs, in vertex order,
\[
\begin{aligned}
&(1,2),(3,4),(27,7),(27,13),(16,5),(6,17),(27,22),(29,8),(30,9),(29,10),\\
&(30,11),(30,12),(30,29),(30,14),(29,15),(30,29),(30,29),(29,18),(30,19),\\
&(29,20),(30,21),(30,29),(30,23),(29,24),(30,25),(29,26),(30,29),(0,28),(0,29),
\end{aligned}
\]
so that $\Vmax=\{0,4\}$, $\Vmin=\{1,2,5\}$ and
$w^\ast(0),\dots,w^\ast(6)=\tfrac12,\tfrac12,\tfrac{23}{64},\tfrac12,\tfrac12,\tfrac{11}{32},\tfrac{33}{64}$.
Take $\sigma:=(0\mapsto2,\ 4\mapsto16)$, whose unique wrong vertex is $0$.
Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item the best responses $\tau,\rho,\tau'$ are unique, the bracket
  $(L,U)$ they generate satisfies $L(1)=\tfrac{11}{32}<\tfrac{23}{64}=w^\ast(2)$,
  and it decides only the Max vertex $4$, at which $\sigma$ is already
  correct, and the Min vertex $5$. A switching iteration that switches the decided wrong
  vertices therefore leaves $\sigma$ unchanged and never terminates,
  although $0$ is strictly switchable at $\sigma$;
\item the decision-rule iteration of \Cref{thm:decide-one-bit}, driven by
  the \emph{same} bracket, terminates on $G$ in four rounds: it retires
  $4$, then $0$ --- which the bracket now decides, the collapse of $4$
  having tightened it --- then $2$ and $5$, and finally abstains with the
  single controlled vertex $1$ left, whose successors $3$ and $4$ both
  have optimal value $\tfrac12$, so that one linear solve returns
  $w^\ast$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The game is stopping and all the quantities named are finite exact
computations: $w^\ast$ by maximisation over the four Max strategies of
the minimum over the eight Min strategies of $v_{\sigma,\tau}$, each
$v_{\sigma,\tau}$ by Gaussian elimination over $\mathbb{Q}$; the three
best-response sets by exhaustive enumeration, each a singleton;
$L:=\max\{\val_\sigma,\val_\rho\}$ and $U:=\val^{\tau'}$ likewise. One
checks $L\le w^\ast\le U$, that $L(1)>U(2)$ and $L(2)>U(1)$ both fail --
- indeed $L(1)=\tfrac{11}{32}$ is already below $w^\ast(2)$, so by
\Cref{prop:bracket}(a) no sound upper bound whatever would decide $0$
from this $L$ --- and that the pairs at $4$ and at $5$ are decided.
Since $\sigma(4)=16$ is the successor the bracket certifies, no switch
occurs. That $0$ is strictly switchable is the computation
$\val_\sigma(1)=\tfrac{5}{16}>0=\val_\sigma(2)$. For (b), retiring a
vertex means retyping it as an average vertex with both edges to the
certified successor, which preserves values by the argument in
\Cref{thm:decide-one-bit}; the four rounds and the final value vector are
again finite exact computations, and the run was repeated $25$ times with
the best-response triple and the auxiliary strategy chosen at random,
terminating every time in four rounds with $w^\ast$ recovered.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
\Cref{prop:pdc-separation} is a refutation of a mechanism, not evidence
about the instance: plain Hoffman--Karp solves $G$ at once, since the
wrong vertex is strictly switchable. Its interest is that the bracket ---
the most natural sound certificate available in polynomial time --- can
be simultaneously too weak to move a switching iteration and strong
enough to drive a decision rule to the answer, on the same game from the
same strategy. Whether some stopping game defeats the decision rule as
well, by leaving a value-distinguishing vertex undecided in every round,
is open; by \Cref{thm:decide-one-bit} a single such instance would settle
the matter, and by \Cref{prop:locality} no bounded-radius inspection can
supply what is missing.
\end{remark}

\subsection{A global mechanism that beats locality}\label{sec:simorder}

\Cref{prop:locality} rules out rules that inspect a bounded neighbourhood.
It does not rule out a rule that inspects the whole game at once in a
purely combinatorial way, and there is a natural one: a simulation
preorder, computed as a single greatest fixed point, which uses no
arithmetic and yet compares values.

\begin{proposition}[The two qualitative sets, inside the model]\label{prop:qualitative}
Let $G$ be an arbitrary SSG, stopping or not.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item $\{v:\val(v)>0\}$ is the least fixed point of
  \[
    Y\;\longmapsto\;\{t_1\}\cup\{v\in\Vmax\cup\Vavg:\ \text{some successor in }Y\}
                      \cup\{v\in\Vmin:\ \text{both successors in }Y\},
  \]
  computed in $O(N^{2})$ operations without arithmetic.
\item For $W\subseteq V$ let $Y(W)$ be the least fixed point of
  \[
    Y\;\longmapsto\;\{t_1\}\cup
    \{v\in W\cap\Vmax: \text{some successor in }Y\}\cup
    \{v\in W\cap\Vmin: \text{both successors in }Y\}\cup
    \{v\in W\cap\Vavg: \text{both successors in }W\text{ and some in }Y\}.
  \]
  Then $\{v:\val(v)=1\}$ is the greatest fixed point of $W\mapsto Y(W)$,
  reached from $W_0:=V$ by $W_{i+1}:=Y(W_i)$ within $N$ steps, in
  $O(N^{3})$ operations without arithmetic.
\end{enumerate}
Neither set depends on the coin biases: the same fixed points describe
the game in which each average vertex takes its second successor with any
probability in $(0,1)$. The one-pass version ``the closed set from which
$t_1$ is reached'' is wrong for (b): with $u\in\Vmin$, $u\to(x,t_1)$ and
$x\in\Vavg$, $x\to(u,u)$ the vertex $u$ has value $0$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(a) Let $\mathrm{rk}(v)$ be the least $i$ with $v$ in the $i$-th iterate.
For $v$ in the fixed point let $\sigma(v)$, at Max vertices, be a
successor of smaller rank; against every $\tau$ the rank drops with
probability at least $\tfrac12$ per step (probability $1$ at Max and Min
vertices, the latter having both successors of smaller rank), so $t_1$ is
reached within $N$ steps with probability at least $2^{-N}$. Conversely
let $U$ be the complement of the fixed point: every Max and average
vertex of $U$ has both successors in $U$ and every Min vertex of $U$ has
one, and $t_1\notin U$; a Min strategy choosing that successor confines
the token to $U$ for ever, so $\val=0$ on $U$.

(b) $Y(W)\subseteq W$ and $Y$ is monotone, so the iterates decrease to
the greatest fixed point $W$. For $v\in W$ define $\sigma(v)$ as in (a)
inside the least fixed point computing $Y(W)$; the token then never leaves
$W$ (both successors of a Min or average vertex of $W$ lie in $W$, and
$\sigma(v)\in W$), and the rank drops with probability at least
$\tfrac12$ per step, so $t_1$ is reached with probability $1$ from every
$v\in W$ under every $\tau$: $\val(v)=1$. Conversely let
$A:=\{\val=1\}$; by Knaster--Tarski it suffices that $A\subseteq Y(A)$.
Suppose $B:=A\setminus Y(A)\neq\emptyset$. An average vertex of $B$ has
both successors in $A$, hence, not being in $Y(A)$, both in $B$; a Min
vertex of $B$ has both successors in $A$ and one in $B$; a Max vertex of
$B$ has every successor that lies in $A$ lying in $B$, and at least one
successor in $A$. Let $c:=\max\{\val(u):u\notin A\}<1$ ($c:=0$ if
$A=V$). Against any $\sigma$ let Min stay in $B$ while the token is in
$B$ and play optimally once it leaves; the token leaves $B$ only at a Max
vertex, into a vertex of value at most $c$, so $\val(v)\le c<1$ for
$v\in B$, a contradiction. The bias claim holds because both arguments
use only the supports of the transitions.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Value-simulation preorder]\label{def:simorder}
Let $G$ be stopping. Let $Z_0:=\{v:w^\ast(v)=0\}$ and
$Z_1:=\{v:w^\ast(v)=1\}$. Both are computable without arithmetic, by the
two fixed points of \Cref{prop:qualitative}: $Z_0$ is the complement of
the least fixed point in (a), one attractor sweep, and $Z_1$ is the
greatest fixed point in (b), a nested iteration --- not a single sweep,
since in $G_8$ below the average vertex $0\to(0,t_1)$ has $w^\ast(0)=1$
although one of its successors is itself. Everything below uses only $Z_0\subseteq\{w^\ast=0\}$ and
$Z_1\subseteq\{w^\ast=1\}$, so any sound under-approximation may be
substituted. On $2^{V\times V}$, ordered by
inclusion, define the monotone map
\[
  \mathcal{F}(R)\;:=\;(Z_0\times V)\cup(V\times Z_1)\cup\{(x,y):C_R(x,y)\},
\]
where $C_R(x,y)$ holds if at least one of the following does.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=2.4em]
\item \emph{(down)} $y$ is a non-sink and: $y\in\Vmax$ and $(x,y^{(j)})\in R$
  for \emph{some} $j$; or $y\in\Vmin\cup\Vavg$ and $(x,y^{(j)})\in R$ for
  \emph{both} $j$.
\item \emph{(up)} $x$ is a non-sink and: $x\in\Vmin$ and $(x^{(i)},y)\in R$
  for \emph{some} $i$; or $x\in\Vmax\cup\Vavg$ and $(x^{(i)},y)\in R$ for
  \emph{both} $i$.
\item \emph{(match)} $x,y$ are non-sinks of the same type and: for
  $\Vmax$, every $i$ admits a $j$ with $(x^{(i)},y^{(j)})\in R$; for
  $\Vmin$, every $j$ admits an $i$ with $(x^{(i)},y^{(j)})\in R$; for
  $\Vavg$, there is a \emph{bijection} $\pi$ with
  $(x^{(i)},y^{(\pi(i))})\in R$ for both $i$.
\end{enumerate}
Write $\preceq\;:=\;\operatorname{gfp}(\mathcal{F})$.
\end{definition}

The (match) clause at $\Vavg$ is the lifting of a relation to two-point
distributions in the sense of probabilistic simulation (from memory,
unchecked against the sources: Jonsson and Larsen; Segala and Lynch), and
the soundness proof below is the standard one for reachability values;
the (up) and (down) clauses are the addition. We call $\preceq$ a
preorder for convenience: reflexivity is proved below, and transitivity,
which nothing below uses, we have not proved.

\begin{theorem}[Soundness]\label{thm:simorder-sound}
Let $G$ be stopping. If $x\preceq y$ then $w^\ast(x)\le w^\ast(y)$.
Moreover $\preceq$ is computable in $O(N^{4})$ arithmetic-free steps, and
one such computation returns a verdict for all $N^{2}$ ordered pairs
simultaneously.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $R:=\preceq=\mathcal{F}(R)$ and $D(y):=\{x:(x,y)\in R\}$. The
diagonal is a post-fixed point --- at a non-sink it satisfies (match),
taking $j=i$, $i=j$ and the identity bijection respectively --- so
$\Delta\subseteq R$ and $y\in D(y)$. Also $t_0\in D(y)$ for every $y$.
Put $f(y):=\max\{w^\ast(x):x\in D(y)\}$, so $w^\ast\le f$ pointwise.

We claim $f\le Tf$. Fix $y$, put $c:=f(y)$ and
$M:=\{x\in D(y):w^\ast(x)=c\}\ne\emptyset$.

First, some $x\in M$ satisfies a clause other than (up). Suppose not.
For $x\in M\cap\Vmin$, clause (up) gives an $i$ with $x^{(i)}\in D(y)$,
so $w^\ast(x^{(i)})\le c$, while
$c=w^\ast(x)=\min_i w^\ast(x^{(i)})\le w^\ast(x^{(i)})$; hence
$w^\ast(x^{(i)})=c$ and $x^{(i)}\in M$. For $x\in M\cap\Vmax$ both
successors lie in $D(y)$, so both have $w^\ast\le c$, and
$w^\ast(x)=\max_i w^\ast(x^{(i)})=c$ forces one of them into $M$. For
$x\in M\cap\Vavg$ both lie in $D(y)$ and their average is $c$, so
\emph{both} lie in $M$. No sink lies in $M$: sinks satisfy neither (up)
nor (match), $t_0\in Z_0$ would put $(t_0,y)$ in the base, and likewise
$t_1$ could only enter through the base. So choosing at each $\Vmax$ and
$\Vmin$ vertex of $M$ a successor in $M$ yields a positional pair under
which the token, started in $M$, never leaves $M$ and hence never reaches
a sink --- contradicting the stopping hypothesis.

So fix $x\in M$ witnessing one of the remaining clauses. If $x\in Z_0$
then $c=0\le(Tf)(y)$. If $y\in Z_1$ then $(Tf)(y)=1\ge c$: for $y=t_1$
this is the definition, and otherwise a $\Vmax$ vertex of $Z_1$ has a
successor in $Z_1$ while a $\Vmin$ or $\Vavg$ vertex of $Z_1$ has both
successors in $Z_1$, where $f\ge w^\ast=1$. If (down) holds then
$x\in D(y^{(j)})$ for some $j$, whence $c\le f(y^{(j)})$, and taking the
maximum, the minimum or the average as the type of $y$ demands gives
$c\le(Tf)(y)$. If (match) holds: for $\Vmax$ pick $i$ with
$w^\ast(x^{(i)})=w^\ast(x)=c$ and $j$ with $x^{(i)}\in D(y^{(j)})$, so
$c\le f(y^{(j)})\le(Tf)(y)$; for $\Vmin$, every $j$ admits an $i$ with
$c=w^\ast(x)\le w^\ast(x^{(i)})\le f(y^{(j)})$, so $c\le\min_j
f(y^{(j)})=(Tf)(y)$; for $\Vavg$, the bijection gives
$w^\ast(x^{(i)})\le f(y^{(\pi(i))})$ for both $i$, and averaging,
\[
  c=\tfrac12\bigl(w^\ast(x^{(0)})+w^\ast(x^{(1)})\bigr)
   \le\tfrac12\bigl(f(y^{(0)})+f(y^{(1)})\bigr)=(Tf)(y),
\]
the bijection being exactly what makes the two coin weights match. This
is the only place where the fairness of the coin is used.

So $f\le Tf$, and $G$ is stopping, so $f\le w^\ast$ by
\Cref{cor:comparison}; with $w^\ast\le f$ this gives $f=w^\ast$. For
$(x,y)\in R$ we get $w^\ast(x)\le f(y)=w^\ast(y)$, which is soundness.
For the cost, $\operatorname{gfp}(\mathcal{F})=\lim_k\mathcal{F}^k(V\times V)$
and the iterates strictly decrease until they stabilise, so there are at
most $N^{2}+1$ rounds, each testing a fixed finite disjunction at each of
$N^{2}$ pairs.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The mechanism beats locality, and still stalls]\label{prop:simorder-stalls}
The preorder $\preceq$ decides pairs that no bounded-radius rule can
decide. In particular, let $G^{\ast}$ be the stopping SSG on non-sink
vertices $0,\dots,16$ with $t_0=17$, $t_1=18$,
$\Vmax=\{0,1,2\}$, $\Vmin=\{3,4,5,6\}$, the rest average, and out-edge
pairs, in vertex order,
\[
\begin{aligned}
&(4,12),(16,15),(13,14),(4,13),(2,16),(8,0),(10,15),(18,0),(0,14),\\
&(17,6),(16,11),(7,13),(7,11),(9,4),(15,7),(5,10),(13,15).
\end{aligned}
\]
All seven controlled vertices of $G^{\ast}$ distinguish the optimal
values of their successors, yet the primal--dual bracket of
\Cref{def:bracket} generated from the all-first-successor strategy ---
under every best-response tie-breaking --- separates none of them; the
preorder $\preceq$ separates six of the seven, including the vertex $0$
that no bracket built on that strategy's lower bound can ever decide
(all quantities exact rationals, ground truth by enumeration of the
$2^{3}\cdot2^{4}$ positional pairs). Nevertheless the decision rule ``return a
controlled vertex whose successors are $\preceq$-comparable, else
abstain'' is not non-stalling in the sense of
\Cref{def:decision-rule}. Let $G_8$ be the stopping SSG on
$\{0,\dots,5\}$ with $t_0=6$, $t_1=7$, types
$(\mathrm{avg},\mathrm{avg},\mathrm{avg},\mathrm{avg},\mathrm{avg},\max)$
and edges
$0\to(0,t_1)$, $1\to(0,t_0)$, $2\to(t_0,4)$, $3\to(0,2)$, $4\to(3,2)$,
$5\to(4,1)$. Then
$w^\ast=(1,\tfrac12,\tfrac15,\tfrac35,\tfrac25,\tfrac12)$, the unique
controlled vertex $5$ has successors of \emph{distinct} optimal values
$\tfrac25$ and $\tfrac12$, and neither $4\preceq1$ nor $1\preceq4$. By
