\Cref{thm:decide-one-bit} the rule therefore yields no polynomial
algorithm.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
$G_8$ is stopping: $t_1$ attracts $0$, $t_0$ attracts $1$ and $2$, then
$0$ attracts $3$, $3$ attracts $4$, and $4,1$ attract $5$. Its values
solve $w^\ast(0)=1$, $w^\ast(1)=\tfrac12$, and
$w^\ast(2)=\tfrac12w^\ast(4)$,
$w^\ast(3)=\tfrac12(1+w^\ast(2))$,
$w^\ast(4)=\tfrac12(w^\ast(3)+w^\ast(2))$, whence
$w^\ast(4)=\tfrac14+\tfrac38w^\ast(4)=\tfrac25$, $w^\ast(2)=\tfrac15$,
$w^\ast(3)=\tfrac35$ and $w^\ast(5)=\max(\tfrac25,\tfrac12)=\tfrac12$.
That $1\not\preceq4$ is \Cref{thm:simorder-sound}, since
$\tfrac12>\tfrac25$. For $4\not\preceq1$: by soundness
$\preceq\;\subseteq\;\mathcal{V}:=\{(x,y):w^\ast(x)\le w^\ast(y)\}$, and
$\preceq=\mathcal{F}(\preceq)\subseteq\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{V})$ by
monotonicity, so it suffices that $(4,1)\notin\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{V})$.
Here $4\notin Z_0$ and $1\notin Z_1$. Clause (down) at the average vertex
$1$ needs both $(4,0)$ and $(4,t_0)$ in $\mathcal{V}$, and the latter
reads $\tfrac25\le0$. Clause (up) at the average vertex $4$ needs both
$(3,1)$ and $(2,1)$, and the former reads $\tfrac35\le\tfrac12$. Clause
(match) needs a bijection: the identity requires $(3,0)$ and $(2,t_0)$,
and $(2,t_0)$ reads $\tfrac15\le0$; the transposition requires
$(3,t_0)$ and $(2,0)$, and $(3,t_0)$ reads $\tfrac35\le0$. All fail.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The obstruction is exact and worth naming. Every clause of
\Cref{def:simorder} matches branches \emph{one to one}: (down) and (up)
compare one vertex with one successor, and (match) pairs successors by a
bijection. At an average vertex the value is a sum of two branches, so a
deficit on one branch may be repaid by a surplus on the other; no
relation built from one-to-one branch matching can see that
compensation, and $G_8$ is a small instance where the repayment is
essential. This is a different failure from
\Cref{prop:locality} --- the preorder is a global computation and does
beat every bounded radius --- and it suggests that a sound comparison
mechanism must at some point add two branches, that is, do arithmetic.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Adding arithmetic: the slack calculus}\label{sec:slack}

The obstruction just identified is repairable, and the repair is the
obvious one: let the two branches of an average vertex share a numerical
budget instead of being matched one to one. Write
$\mathrm{ag}^{+}_{v}$ for $\max$, $\min$ or the mean according to the
type of $v$, and $\mathrm{ag}^{-}_{v}$ for its dual ($\min$, $\max$, the
mean).

\begin{definition}[Slack calculus]\label{def:slack}
Let $G$ be an SSG and $Z_0,Z_1$ as in \Cref{def:simorder}. Define
$\Delta_0\equiv1$ on $V\times V$ and let $\Delta_{k+1}(x,y)$ be the
minimum, clamped to $[-1,1]$, of $1$ together with every applicable
entry among: $0$ if $x\in Z_0$; $0$ if $y\in Z_1$; $-1$ if both; $0$ if
$x=y$;
\[
  \mathrm{ag}^{-}_{y}\bigl(\Delta_k(x,y^{(0)}),\Delta_k(x,y^{(1)})\bigr)
  \quad(y\text{ a non-sink}),\qquad
  \mathrm{ag}^{+}_{x}\bigl(\Delta_k(x^{(0)},y),\Delta_k(x^{(1)},y)\bigr)
  \quad(x\text{ a non-sink}),
\]
and, when $x,y$ are non-sinks of the same type, the composite of the two,
where for $\Vavg$ the composite is
$\tfrac12(\Delta_k(x^{(0)},y^{(\pi(0))})+\Delta_k(x^{(1)},y^{(\pi(1))}))$
for either bijection $\pi$. The intended reading of $\Delta_k(x,y)$ is
``$w^\ast(x)\le w^\ast(y)+\Delta_k(x,y)$'', so a negative entry decides
the pair.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:slack-grade}
Read over the two-element grade set: with $\chi_R(x,y):=0$ for $(x,y)\in R$
and $1$ otherwise, one round of the clauses above applied to $\chi_R$ and
rounded up to $\{0,1\}$ is the indicator of $\mathcal F(R)$ with the
diagonal adjoined, $\mathcal F$ the operator of \Cref{def:simorder} --- in
$\{0,1\}$ a minimum is $0$ iff some argument is, a maximum or a mean iff
both are, which is exactly clauses (i)--(iii) provided both composites
of the two one-sided clauses are among the clauses and the minimum is
taken, as the calculus is run --- and the greatest fixed
point of that iteration from the all-related relation is $\preceq$. So
\Cref{def:simorder} is this calculus over $\{0,1\}$ read coinductively,
and \Cref{def:slack} is the same clause set over the dyadic grades read
inductively (the round-$17$ oracle-barrier route; an unfolding of the two
definitions, in the sense of the abstract interpretation of the remark
after \Cref{cor:grid-iteration}). The two readings are not
interchangeable: iterating the bare clause map from the all-related
relation converges to $\preceq$, while the running minimum with the
current state that \Cref{def:slack} keeps would freeze at once at the
unsound all-related state.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Soundness and cost]\label{thm:slack-sound}
For every SSG, every $k$ and all $x,y$: $\Delta_k(x,y)\ge
w^\ast(x)-w^\ast(y)$, and $\Delta_{k+1}\le\Delta_k$. Every entry of
$\Delta_k$ lies in $2^{-k}\mathbb{Z}\cap[-1,1]$, so $k$ rounds cost
$O(k)$ bits per entry and $O(kN^{2})$ rational operations. No stopping
hypothesis is needed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $D(x,y):=w^\ast(x)-w^\ast(y)$. Each clause is \emph{exact} at $D$.
For the (down) clause, $Tw^\ast=w^\ast$ (\Cref{thm:lfp-general}) gives
$w^\ast(y)=\mathrm{ag}^{+}_{y}(w^\ast(y^{(j)}))$, and since
$c-\max=\min(c-\cdot)$, $c-\min=\max(c-\cdot)$ and $c-\mathrm{mean}
=\mathrm{mean}(c-\cdot)$, we get
$D(x,y)=\mathrm{ag}^{-}_{y}(D(x,y^{(j)}))$. For (up), $\max$, $\min$ and
the mean all commute with adding a constant, so
$D(x,y)=\mathrm{ag}^{+}_{x}(D(x^{(i)},y))$. The composite clause is the
two in succession, and for two average vertices either bijection gives
$D(x,y)=\tfrac12\sum_i(w^\ast(x^{(i)})-w^\ast(y^{(\pi(i))}))$. The bases
are immediate from $Z_0\subseteq\{w^\ast=0\}$ and
$Z_1\subseteq\{w^\ast=1\}$, and $D\in[-1,1]$. So every clause maps $D$ to
$D$; as all clauses are monotone and $\Delta_0\equiv1\ge D$, induction
gives $\Delta_k\ge D$ for every $k$, and $\Delta_1\le\Delta_0$ propagates
to $\Delta_{k+1}\le\Delta_k$. For the bit-size, constants are integral,
$\min$ and $\max$ preserve denominators, and a mean of two elements of
$2^{-k}\mathbb{Z}$ lies in $2^{-(k+1)}\mathbb{Z}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The repair works]\label{prop:slack-repairs}
On the game $G_8$ of \Cref{prop:simorder-stalls}, where the
arithmetic-free preorder stalls, $\Delta_7(4,1)=-\tfrac1{32}<0$, which
certifies $w^\ast(4)<w^\ast(1)$ and decides the unique controlled vertex.
The successive values of $\Delta_k(4,1)$ are
$1,\tfrac12,\tfrac14,\tfrac18,\tfrac1{16},0,-\tfrac1{32}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Exact rational computation from $\Delta_0\equiv1$, with the values
$w^\ast=(1,\tfrac12,\tfrac15,\tfrac35,\tfrac25,\tfrac12)$ of
\Cref{prop:simorder-stalls}.
\end{proof}

So branch compensation is genuinely repaired. The calculus nevertheless
buys nothing, for a reason that has nothing to do with precision: on
separated parts of the game it is exactly two-sided value iteration.
Write $u_k:=T^{k}\mathbf 1$ and $l_k:=T^{k}\mathbf 0$.

\begin{theorem}[The slack calculus is dominated by value iteration]\label{thm:slack-barrier}
Let $G$ be stopping and let $A,B\subseteq V\setminus\{t_0,t_1\}$ satisfy
(i) $A\cap B=\emptyset$; (ii) each of $A$, $B$ contains the non-sink
successors of its members; (iii) $(A\cup B)\cap(Z_0\cup Z_1)=\emptyset$.
Then for all $k\ge0$ and all
$(x,y)\in(A\cup\{t_0,t_1\})\times(B\cup\{t_0,t_1\})$,
\[
  \Delta_k(x,y)\;\ge\;u_k(x)-l_k(y).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put $E_k(x,y):=u_k(x)-l_k(y)$; then $E_0\equiv1=\Delta_0$. By (ii) the
region is closed under the clauses, and each clause is monotone, so by
induction it suffices to check that every applicable clause applied to
$E_k$ is at least $E_{k+1}$. The value $1$ dominates $E_{k+1}$ since
$u,l\in\mathcal{X}$. By (iii) a member of $A\cup\{t_0,t_1\}$ lying in
$Z_0$ must be $t_0$, where the clause yields $0\ge-l_{k+1}(y)=E_{k+1}$;
dually for $Z_1$ and $y=t_1$; at $(t_0,t_1)$ the clause gives $-1=E_{k+1}$.
By (i) $x=y$ forces both to be the same sink, where $E_{k+1}=0$. For
(down),
$\mathrm{ag}^{-}_{y}(E_k(x,y^{(j)}))=u_k(x)-\mathrm{ag}^{+}_{y}(l_k(y^{(j)}))
=u_k(x)-l_{k+1}(y)\ge u_{k+1}(x)-l_{k+1}(y)$, using $u_{k+1}\le u_k$; for
(up), $\mathrm{ag}^{+}_{x}(E_k(x^{(i)},y))=u_{k+1}(x)-l_k(y)\ge E_{k+1}$,
using $l_{k+1}\ge l_k$; and the composite clause evaluates to exactly
$u_{k+1}(x)-l_{k+1}(y)$. Clamping only raises the value.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[An exponential stall]\label{cor:slack-stalls}
For $m\ge3$ let $H_m$ be $G_m$ of \Cref{thm:vi-lower} together with a
fresh average vertex $h\to\{t_0,t_1\}$ and a fresh Max vertex
$v\to\{c_1,h\}$, so that $N=2m+4$ and $v$ is the only controlled vertex.
Then $w^\ast(h)=\tfrac12$ and $w^\ast(c_1)=\tfrac12+2^{-(m+1)}$ differ,
yet $\Delta_k(h,c_1)>0$ and $\Delta_k(c_1,h)>0$ for every
$k<\ln2\cdot2^{m-2}$. The pair test at $v$ therefore stalls on $H_m$ for
$2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds. The own-successor test of
\Cref{rem:own-successor}, which compares $v$ with $c_1$ and with $h$, is
\emph{not} covered by \Cref{thm:slack-barrier}: every successor-closed
set containing $v$ meets both $A$ and $B$ below. Measured in exact
arithmetic it first fires at rounds $15,39,101,250$ for $m=3,\dots,6$,
one round after the pair entries first decide; we have no proof of a
bound for it, and the exponential stall of the slack calculus as a
\emph{decision rule} is, on this family, a measurement.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Take $A=\{h\}$ and $B=\{c_1,\dots,c_{2m}\}$: both are closed under
non-sink successors, they are disjoint, and by \Cref{thm:vi-lower}(i),(ii)
no member has value $0$ or $1$, so (iii) holds. Since $h\to\{t_0,t_1\}$
we have $u_k(h)=l_k(h)=\tfrac12$ for $k\ge2$ (the iterates start from the
unpinned constants, so $u_1(h)=1$ and $l_1(h)=0$; at $k=1$ the bound below
is $1-0>0$ anyway). By
\Cref{thm:slack-barrier}, $\Delta_k(h,c_1)\ge\tfrac12-l_k(c_1)>0$ for
$k<\ln2\cdot2^{m-2}$ by \Cref{thm:vi-lower}(iii), and
$\Delta_k(c_1,h)\ge u_k(c_1)-\tfrac12\ge w^\ast(c_1)-\tfrac12=2^{-(m+1)}>0$,
using $u_k\ge w^\ast$. Neither sign decides the pair. Verified in exact
rational arithmetic for $m=3,4,5$, together with the own-successor rounds
stated, which \Cref{rem:own-successor} reports.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The slack calculus is dominated by two-sided value iteration]\label{thm:slack-vi-upper}
Let $G$ be an arbitrary SSG, let $\Delta_k$ be as in \Cref{def:slack}, and put
$u_k:=T^{k}\mathbf 1$ and $l_k:=T^{k}\mathbf 0$, the iterates taken on all of
$V$ from $u_0\equiv1$ and $l_0\equiv0$. Then for every $k\ge0$ and all
$x,y\in V$, sinks included,
\[
  \Delta_{2k}(x,y)\;\le\;u_k(x)-l_k(y).
\]
No stopping hypothesis is needed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Induction on $k$. For $k=0$ both sides are $1$. Assume the bound at $k$. One
slack round applies the $(\mathrm{up})$ clause at $x$, the next applies the
$(\mathrm{down})$ clause at $y$, and we use them in that order.

Let $x$ be a non-sink. Since $\mathrm{ag}^{+}_{x}$ is monotone and commutes with
adding a constant,
\[
  \Delta_{2k+1}(x,y)\le
  \mathrm{ag}^{+}_{x}\bigl(\Delta_{2k}(x^{(0)},y),\Delta_{2k}(x^{(1)},y)\bigr)
  \le\mathrm{ag}^{+}_{x}\bigl(u_k(x^{(0)}),u_k(x^{(1)})\bigr)-l_k(y)
  =u_{k+1}(x)-l_k(y),
\]
because $\mathrm{ag}^{+}$ is $\max$ at $\Vmax$, $\min$ at $\Vmin$ and the mean
at $\Vavg$, which is exactly the action of $T$. If $x=t_0$ the
$(\mathrm{up})$ clause is unavailable, but $\Delta$ is nonincreasing in $k$ ---
the clauses are monotone and $\Delta_1\le\Delta_0$ --- so
$\Delta_{2k+1}(t_0,y)\le\Delta_{2k}(t_0,y)\le u_k(t_0)-l_k(y)$, and
$u_k(t_0)=u_{k+1}(t_0)=0$ for $k\ge1$ while for $k=0$ the base clause gives
$\Delta_1(t_0,y)\le0=u_1(t_0)-l_0(y)$. The case $x=t_1$ is the same with
$u_k(t_1)=1$.

Now let $y$ be a non-sink. By the same two properties of
$\mathrm{ag}^{-}_{y}$, and because $\mathrm{ag}^{-}_{y}(c-a,c-b)=c-(Tl)(y)$
when $(a,b)=(l(y^{(0)}),l(y^{(1)}))$ --- $\min$ against $\max$ at $\Vmax$,
$\max$ against $\min$ at $\Vmin$, the mean against itself at $\Vavg$ ---
\[
  \Delta_{2k+2}(x,y)\le
  \mathrm{ag}^{-}_{y}\bigl(\Delta_{2k+1}(x,y^{(0)}),\Delta_{2k+1}(x,y^{(1)})\bigr)
  \le u_{k+1}(x)-l_{k+1}(y).
\]
For $y=t_1$ the $(\mathrm{down})$ clause is unavailable; there the
$(\mathrm{up})$ clause at $x$ already gives
$\Delta_{2k+2}(x,t_1)\le\mathrm{ag}^{+}_{x}(u_{k+1}(x^{(i)}))-1=u_{k+2}(x)-1
\le u_{k+1}(x)-l_{k+1}(t_1)$, using $u_{k+2}\le u_{k+1}$; and $y=t_0$ is
symmetric.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:slack-sandwich}
\Cref{thm:slack-barrier} bounds $\Delta_k$ from \emph{below} by
$u_k(x)-l_k(y)$, but only on two disjoint parts closed under non-sink
successors and avoiding $Z_0\cup Z_1$. \Cref{thm:slack-vi-upper} bounds it from
\emph{above} by the same quantity, everywhere and with no hypothesis at all. So
on the configurations the first covers, the slack calculus \emph{is} two-sided
value iteration, to within a factor two in the number of rounds; and everywhere
else it is at least as fast, but never faster than value iteration in this
measure. This settles the plain calculus of \Cref{def:slack} in both
directions, and it is exactly what the transitivity clause of
\Cref{def:trans-slack} escapes: the bound above uses only $(\mathrm{up})$ and
$(\mathrm{down})$, and \Cref{prop:trans-Hm} shows the closure beats it by an
exponential factor on $H_m$. We checked the inequality on $300$ small random games,
$207$ of them non-stopping, at all $N^{2}$ pairs and for $k\le6$ (the
generator's size range was not recorded): no violation, and $57{,}353$
instances of equality, so it is attained often and is not slack.
\end{remark}



\medskip
The barrier just proved is a statement about the clauses of
\Cref{def:slack}, and it is sharp for them. It does not survive the
addition of a single further clause, and the corollary above does not
either. Recall that the premises of transitivity leave any separated
region, so \Cref{thm:slack-barrier} says nothing about it.

\begin{definition}[Transitive slack calculus]\label{def:trans-slack}
Write $L(W)$ for one application of the clauses of \Cref{def:slack} to a
matrix $W$, and let
\[
  C(W)(x,y):=\min\Bigl\{\textstyle\sum_{i<r}W(z_i,z_{i+1})\ :\
    z_0=x,\ z_r=y\Bigr\}
\]
be the min-plus (all-pairs shortest path) closure, which is exactly the
closure of $W$ under the clause
\emph{(trans)} $\Delta(x,y)\le\Delta(x,z)+\Delta(z,y)$. It is well
defined --- the minimum over sequences of every length is attained on a
simple path, which is what Floyd--Warshall computes --- when $W$ has no
negative cycle, and every $W\ge D$ has none, since each cycle sum of $D$
telescopes to $0$; that is the case throughout, by
\Cref{thm:trans-sound}. Put
$\Delta^{T}_0\equiv1$ and
$\Delta^{T}_{k+1}:=\operatorname{clamp}_{[-1,1]}C\bigl(L(\Delta^{T}_k)\bigr)$.
The reading is unchanged: $\Delta^{T}_k(x,y)$ certifies
$w^\ast(x)\le w^\ast(y)+\Delta^{T}_k(x,y)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Soundness and cost]\label{thm:trans-sound}
For every SSG, every $k$ and all $x,y$:
$\Delta^{T}_k(x,y)\ge w^\ast(x)-w^\ast(y)$, and
$\Delta^{T}_{k+1}\le\Delta^{T}_k\le\Delta_k$. Every entry lies in
$2^{-k}\mathbb{Z}\cap[-1,1]$, so $k$ rounds cost $O(k)$ bits per entry
and $O(kN^{3})$ rational operations. No stopping hypothesis is needed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $D(x,y):=w^\ast(x)-w^\ast(y)$. By the proof of
\Cref{thm:slack-sound} every clause of $L$ is exact at $D$, and
$D(x,z)+D(z,y)=D(x,y)$ identically, so every path sum in $C$ telescopes
to $D(x,y)$ and $C$ is exact at $D$ too. All operations are monotone and
$\Delta^{T}_0\equiv1\ge D$, so induction gives $\Delta^{T}_k\ge D$;
$\Delta^{T}_1\le\Delta^{T}_0$ then propagates. Taking the single-arc path
gives $C(W)\le W$, whence $\Delta^{T}_k\le\Delta_k$. The set
$2^{-k}\mathbb Z$ is closed under addition, $\max$ and $\min$, and a mean
of two of its elements lies in $2^{-(k+1)}\mathbb Z$; the closure adds at
most $N-1$ such numbers. Floyd--Warshall costs $O(N^{3})$ per round.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[\Cref{cor:slack-stalls} fails for
$\Delta^{T}$]\label{prop:trans-Hm}
On the family $H_m$ of \Cref{cor:slack-stalls} the first round at which
$\Delta^{T}_k$ decides the pair $\{h,c_1\}$ is
\[
  k=9,\,13,\,17,\,21,\,25,\,29,\,33\quad(m=3,\dots,9),\qquad k=38\quad(m=10),
\]
that is $k=4m-3$ up to $m=9$, and linear in $N=2m+4$; whereas the
calculus of \Cref{def:slack} first decides it at
$k=14,38,100,249,596$ for $m=3,\dots,7$ --- in each case exactly one more
than the number of value-iteration steps recorded in
\Cref{thm:vi-lower}(iii), as \Cref{thm:slack-barrier} predicts. The exponential stall of \Cref{cor:slack-stalls} is therefore
destroyed by the transitivity clause.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Exact rational computation of $\Delta_k$ and $\Delta^{T}_k$ from
\Cref{def:slack} and \Cref{def:trans-slack}; soundness was checked at
every round against $D$ on all $N^{2}$ pairs, with no violation. For
$m=3$ the sequence $\Delta^{T}_k(h,c_1)$, $k=1,\dots,9$, is
\[
  1,\ \tfrac12,\ \tfrac12,\ \tfrac38,\ \tfrac14,\ \tfrac18,\ \tfrac3{64},\ 0,\ -\tfrac7{256}.
\]
The mechanism is visible in the residual $\Delta_k-D$: for the plain
calculus the row of $h$ obeys the two-sided value iteration of
\Cref{thm:slack-barrier} and decays at the mixing rate of $G_m$, while
(trans) routes the comparison through the pairs $(c_i,c_j)$, which couple
at once because $c_i$ and $c_j$ share the successor $c_1$ for
$i,j\le m-1$, so the composite clause pairs the shared branches and
halves their residual every round.
\end{proof}

The transitivity clause is not merely faster here. The calculus is in
fact complete on the stopping class, so every stall --- with or without
transitivity --- is a statement about the number of rounds and never
about what the mechanism can express.

\begin{theorem}[Exact limit on stopping games]\label{thm:trans-complete}
Let $G$ be stopping and let $M:=\lim_k\Delta_k$, respectively
$\lim_k\Delta^{T}_k$; the limits exist because the sequences are
nonincreasing and bounded below by $D$, and they are fixed points of the
respective clause systems, all clauses being continuous. Then
$M(x,y)=w^\ast(x)-w^\ast(y)$ for all $x,y$. Only \emph{(up)},
\emph{(down)} and the bases are used, so this holds equally for
\Cref{def:slack}, \Cref{def:trans-slack} and \Cref{def:seeded}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
$M\ge D$ by \Cref{thm:slack-sound}. The diagonal clause gives
$M(t_0,t_0)=M(t_1,t_1)=0$; the base gives $M(t_0,t_1)\le-1$, and
$M\ge D$ gives $M(t_0,t_1)=-1$ and $M(t_1,t_0)=1$.

\emph{Step 1.} Fix $y$ and put $h(v):=M(v,y)$. Since $M$ is a fixed point
of the clause system, the \emph{(up)} clause gives
$h(v)\le\mathrm{ag}^{+}_{v}\bigl(h(v^{(i)})\bigr)$ at every non-sink $v$,
that is $h\le\widetilde Th$, where $\widetilde T$ is the Shapley operator
of $G$ with the two sinks paying $h(t_0)$ and $h(t_1)$. Now $t_0\in Z_0$
gives $h(t_0)=M(t_0,y)\le0$, while $h(t_1)=M(t_1,y)\ge D(t_1,y)=1-w^\ast(y)\ge0$;
so $\beta:=h(t_1)-h(t_0)\ge0$ and the affine map
$t\mapsto h(t_0)+\beta t$ is nondecreasing, hence commutes with $\max$,
$\min$ and the mean. Therefore $g:=h(t_0)+\beta w^\ast$ satisfies
$\widetilde Tg=g$, because $Tw^\ast=w^\ast$ (\Cref{thm:lfp-general}) and
the sinks receive exactly $h(t_0)$ and $h(t_0)+\beta$. As $G$ is stopping,
$\widetilde T$ has a unique fixed point and every orbit converges to it:
\Cref{lem:absorption} and \Cref{thm:contraction} use only that the sinks
are absorbing and pay constants, not that those constants are $0$ and
$1$ (\Cref{rem:terminals}); when $\beta=0$ the fixed point is the
constant vector $h(t_0)$ and nothing below is needed. From $h\le\widetilde Th$ and monotonicity, $h\le\widetilde T^{k}h\to g$,
so
\[
  M(v,y)\ \le\ M(t_0,y)\bigl(1-w^\ast(v)\bigr)+M(t_1,y)\,w^\ast(v).
\]
Taking $y=t_0$ gives $M(v,t_0)\le w^\ast(v)$, and $y=t_1$ gives
$M(v,t_1)\le w^\ast(v)-1$.

\emph{Step 2.} Fix $x$ and put $f(v):=-M(x,v)$. The \emph{(down)} clause
gives $M(x,v)\le\mathrm{ag}^{-}_{v}\bigl(M(x,v^{(j)})\bigr)$, that is
$f(v)\ge\mathrm{ag}^{+}_{v}\bigl(f(v^{(j)})\bigr)$, so $f\ge\widehat Tf$
with the sinks paying $f(t_0)=-M(x,t_0)$ and $f(t_1)=-M(x,t_1)$. By
soundness $M(x,t_0)\ge D(x,t_0)=w^\ast(x)$ and by Step~1
$M(x,t_1)\le w^\ast(x)-1$, so $f(t_1)\ge f(t_0)$ and the same affine
argument applies; $f\ge\widehat T^{k}f$ converges to the fixed point, giving
\[
  M(x,v)\ \le\ M(x,t_0)\bigl(1-w^\ast(v)\bigr)+M(x,t_1)\,w^\ast(v)
  \ \le\ w^\ast(x)\bigl(1-w^\ast(v)\bigr)+\bigl(w^\ast(x)-1\bigr)w^\ast(v)
  \ =\ w^\ast(x)-w^\ast(v),
\]
the middle inequality by Step~1 and $1-w^\ast(v),w^\ast(v)\ge0$. So
$M\le D$, and with $M\ge D$, $M=D$.
\end{proof}

Off the stopping class the picture is the opposite, and it has an exact
description: the calculus cannot see past \emph{any} fixed point of $T$,
not merely the least one.

\begin{lemma}[Fixed points certify stalls]\label{lem:phi-certificate}
Let $G$ be an arbitrary SSG and let $\varphi\in[0,1]^{V}$ satisfy
$T\varphi=\varphi$, $\varphi=0$ on $Z_0$ and $\varphi=1$ on $Z_1$. Then
$\Delta^{T}_k(x,y)\ge\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)$ for every $k$ and all $x,y$;
a fortiori the same holds for $\Delta_k$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Put $M(x,y):=\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)\in[-1,1]$ and check that $M$ is at
most every clause value at $M$; the claim then follows by induction, all
clauses being monotone and $\Delta^{T}_0\equiv1\ge M$. The constant $1$
dominates $M$ since $\varphi\in[0,1]$. If $x\in Z_0$ the clause value is
$0\ge-\varphi(y)=M(x,y)$; if $y\in Z_1$ it is $0\ge\varphi(x)-1=M(x,y)$;
if both, it is $-1=M(x,y)$; the diagonal clause gives $0=M(x,x)$. For
\emph{(down)},
$\mathrm{ag}^{-}_{y}(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y^{(j)}))=\varphi(x)-(T\varphi)(y)=M(x,y)$,
and for \emph{(up)},
$\mathrm{ag}^{+}_{x}(\varphi(x^{(i)})-\varphi(y))=(T\varphi)(x)-\varphi(y)=M(x,y)$;
the composite is the two in succession, and either bijection at two
average vertices gives $M(x,y)$ as well. Finally
$M(x,z)+M(z,y)=M(x,y)$, so \emph{(trans)} and every path sum of $C$ are
exact at $M$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[A permanent stall, and it is not
stopping]\label{prop:trans-stall}
Let $A_0$ be the SSG on non-sink vertices $a,p,q,u$ with
$a\to(t_0,t_1)$ and $q\to(a,t_1)$ average, $p\to(a,p)$ and $u\to(p,q)$
in $\Vmax$. Then $w^\ast=(\tfrac12,\tfrac12,\tfrac34,\tfrac34)$, so $u$
is value-distinguishing and Max must move to $q$; but
$\Delta^{T}_k(p,q)=\Delta^{T}_k(q,p)=\tfrac14$ for all $k\ge5$, so no
$k$ decides $u$. The obstruction is exactly
\Cref{lem:phi-certificate}: $\varphi=(\tfrac12,1,\tfrac34,1)$ satisfies
$T\varphi=\varphi$ and gives $\Delta^{T}_k(p,q)\ge\tfrac14$, while
$D(p,q)=-\tfrac14$. Damping removes the stall: on $(A_0)_m$ of
\Cref{def:damping} the pair is decided at $k=7,8,10$ for $m=2,3,4$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
$A_0$ is not stopping, since $\{p\}$ is a trap (\Cref{lem:trapchar}).
The values are immediate: $w^\ast(a)=\tfrac12$, $w^\ast(q)=\tfrac34$,
$w^\ast(p)=\lfp$ of $t\mapsto\max(\tfrac12,t)$, which is $\tfrac12$, and
$w^\ast(u)=\max(\tfrac12,\tfrac34)=\tfrac34$. That $\varphi$ is a fixed
point is a one-line check: $\max(\tfrac12,1)=1$ at $p$ and at $u$,
$\tfrac12(0+1)=\tfrac12$ at $a$, $\tfrac12(\tfrac12+1)=\tfrac34$ at $q$.
\Cref{lem:phi-certificate} then gives $\Delta^{T}_k(p,q)\ge\tfrac14>0$,
and soundness gives $\Delta^{T}_k(q,p)\ge D(q,p)=\tfrac14>0$. The
displayed equalities and the damped counts are exact computations.
\end{proof}

\Cref{lem:phi-certificate} is the only certificate of its kind, and that
fact explains both the scope of \Cref{thm:slack-barrier} and the fact
that \Cref{prop:trans-stall} had to leave the stopping class.

\begin{definition}[Separable schedule]\label{def:separable}
A sequence $(M_k)_{k\ge0}$ of matrices on $V\times V$ is \emph{separable}
if $M_k(x,y)=\varphi_k(x)-\psi_k(y)$ for functions
$\varphi_k,\psi_k\colon V\to\mathbb{R}$. It is \emph{admissible} if
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item $M_0\le1$ and, for every $k$, $M_{k+1}$ is at most every clause of
      \Cref{def:slack} --- the constant $1$, the four bases, \emph{(up)},
      \emph{(down)} and the composite --- evaluated at $M_k$; and
\item every $M_{k+1}$ satisfies
      $M_{k+1}(x,y)\le M_{k+1}(x,z)+M_{k+1}(z,y)$ for all $x,y,z$.
\end{enumerate}
Condition (a) alone is what an induction proving $M_k\le\Delta_k$
requires; (b) is what the closure $C$ of \Cref{def:trans-slack} adds.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:separable-lower}
An admissible sequence, separable or not, satisfies
$M_k\le\Delta^{T}_k$ for every $k$; under (a) alone it satisfies
$M_k\le\Delta_k$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Induction, with $M_0\le1=\Delta^{T}_0$. Suppose $M_k\le\Delta^{T}_k$.
Every clause of \Cref{def:slack} is monotone, so applying them to
$\Delta^{T}_k$ gives entrywise at least what they give at $M_k$, and (a)
yields $M_{k+1}\le L(\Delta^{T}_k)$. By (b), for every path
$x=z_0,\dots,z_r=y$,
\[
  M_{k+1}(x,y)\ \le\ \sum_{i<r}M_{k+1}(z_i,z_{i+1})
              \ \le\ \sum_{i<r}L(\Delta^{T}_k)(z_i,z_{i+1}),
\]
so $M_{k+1}\le C(L(\Delta^{T}_k))$. Finally $M_{k+1}\le1$ by the constant
clause, so $M_{k+1}\le\min\bigl(1,C(L(\Delta^{T}_k))\bigr)$, and clamping
from below only increases, so $M_{k+1}\le\Delta^{T}_{k+1}$. The statement
for $\Delta_k$ is the same argument without the closure step.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Separable certificates are exactly the fixed points of
$T$]\label{thm:separable}
Let $G$ be an SSG and $(M_k)$ an admissible separable sequence. Then for
every $k\ge1$ the two functions coincide, $\varphi_k=\psi_k$, and,
normalising $\varphi_k(t_0)=0$,
\[
  \varphi_k(t_1)=1,\qquad
  (T\varphi_k)(v)=\varphi_k(v)\ \text{ for every non-sink }v .
\]
If $G$ is stopping, then $\varphi_k=w^\ast$ and hence
\[
  M_k(x,y)\;=\;w^\ast(x)-w^\ast(y)\qquad(k\ge1),
\]
which by \Cref{thm:trans-complete} is precisely the limit of
$\Delta^{T}_k$. Consequently no admissible separable sequence can witness
that $\Delta^{T}_k$ is bounded away from $w^\ast(x)-w^\ast(y)$ on a
stopping game, at any round.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Fix $k\ge0$ and abbreviate $\varphi:=\varphi_{k+1}$, $\psi:=\psi_{k+1}$.
The base clause ``$0$ if $x=y$'' and (a) give $M_{k+1}(x,x)\le0$, that is
$\varphi\le\psi$. Condition (b) with $y:=x$ gives, for every $z$,
\[
  \varphi(x)-\psi(x)\;=\;M_{k+1}(x,x)\;\le\;M_{k+1}(x,z)+M_{k+1}(z,x)
  \;=\;\bigl(\varphi(x)-\psi(x)\bigr)+\bigl(\varphi(z)-\psi(z)\bigr),
\]
so $\psi\le\varphi$. Hence $\varphi_{k+1}=\psi_{k+1}$ for every $k\ge0$.

Now let $k\ge1$, so that $\psi_k=\varphi_k$, and let $x$ be a non-sink.
Because $\max$, $\min$ and the mean commute with adding a constant, the
\emph{(up)} clause of \Cref{def:slack} evaluated at $M_k$ and at the pair
$(x,x)$ equals
$\mathrm{ag}^{+}_{x}\bigl(\varphi_k(x^{(i)})-\psi_k(x)\bigr)
=(T\varphi_k)(x)-\varphi_k(x)$, where $T$ is formed with the sink values
$\varphi_k(t_0)$ and $\varphi_k(t_1)$; and the \emph{(down)} clause at
$(x,x)$ equals $\varphi_k(x)-(T\psi_k)(x)=\varphi_k(x)-(T\varphi_k)(x)$,
by $c-\max=\min(c-\cdot)$, $c-\min=\max(c-\cdot)$ and
$c-\mathrm{mean}=\mathrm{mean}(c-\cdot)$. Condition (a) applied to each,
together with $M_{k+1}(x,x)=0$, gives
\[
  0\le(T\varphi_k)(x)-\varphi_k(x)
  \qquad\text{and}\qquad
  0\le\varphi_k(x)-(T\varphi_k)(x),
\]
so $T\varphi_k=\varphi_k$ at every non-sink. Since $M_k$ depends only on
differences of $\varphi_k$ we may normalise $\varphi_k(t_0)=0$. Taking
$x=t_0\in Z_0$ and $y=t_1\in Z_1$, the base clause $-1$ and (a) give
$\varphi_k(t_0)-\varphi_k(t_1)\le-1$, and the constant clause at
$(t_1,t_0)$ gives $\varphi_k(t_1)-\varphi_k(t_0)\le1$; so
$\varphi_k(t_1)=1$.

If $G$ is stopping, $\varphi_k$ is a fixed point of $T^{0,1}=T$ lying in
$[-1,1]^{V}$ --- the constant clause at the pairs $(x,t_0)$ and
$(t_0,x)$ gives $\varphi_k(x)\le1$ and $-\varphi_k(x)\le1$ ---
so $\varphi_k=w^\ast$ by \Cref{lem:gen-comparison} with $\alpha=0$,
$\beta=1$, and $M_k(x,y)=w^\ast(x)-w^\ast(y)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:separable}
Five consequences; (a)--(c) are proved below, (d) and (e) are explained
in place.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item The certificate $E_k(x,y):=u_k(x)-l_k(y)$ of
      \Cref{thm:slack-barrier} \emph{does} satisfy the triangle
      inequality, since
      $E_k(x,z)+E_k(z,y)-E_k(x,y)=u_k(z)-l_k(z)\ge0$. What it violates is
      the diagonal base of \Cref{def:slack}: $E_k(x,x)=u_k(x)-l_k(x)>0$
      wherever value iteration has not yet converged at $x$. That is
      exactly why \Cref{thm:slack-barrier} is stated for two
      \emph{disjoint} successor-closed parts, on which the diagonal never
      arises; and by \Cref{thm:separable} the restriction cannot be
      removed.
\item \Cref{lem:phi-certificate} exhibits the general separable
      certificate, and \Cref{thm:separable} says it is the only one. On a
      stopping game it therefore says no more than
      \Cref{thm:trans-complete} already does, and a permanent stall such
      as \Cref{prop:trans-stall}'s must occur, as it does, on a game that
      is not stopping --- where $T$ has a second fixed point.
\item Any proof that $\Delta^{T}$ needs superpolynomially many rounds on
      stopping games must use a certificate that is not of the form
      $\varphi(x)-\psi(y)$.
\item The same applies verbatim to the hybrid of \Cref{def:hybrid}: one
      of its rounds applies $\mathcal L$ and then $\mathcal C$, so an
      admissible separable sequence for it is admissible in the sense of
      \Cref{def:separable}. This is the structural reason behind
      \Cref{rem:hybrid-barrier-lift}, which records the same failure for
      one family and attributes it to \Cref{lem:transport-exact}: no box
      certificate can bound the hybrid, whatever the game.
\item What \Cref{thm:hybrid-convex-barrier} does to escape is to zero
      the diagonal. Its certificates are the support-function differences
      $\Lambda(P)(x,y)=\max_{z\in P}(z(x)-z(y))$, which satisfy
      $\Lambda(P)(x,x)=0$ and are therefore not separable, the two
      conditions of \Cref{def:separable} that forced the collapse being
      met for a different reason. The repair is not free: zeroing the
      diagonal of $u_k(x)-l_k(y)$ restores \Cref{def:separable}(b) and
      the diagonal base but not the clause conditions, and it cannot,
      since \Cref{prop:trans-Hm} exhibits a stopping game on which
      $\Delta^{T}$ decides in $4m-3$ rounds while $u_k-l_k$ stays
      positive for $2^{\Omega(N)}$. The disjointness hypothesis of
      \Cref{thm:slack-barrier} is doing real work.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
(a) is the displayed identity together with
$\Delta_k(x,x)\le0$, which holds for $k\ge1$ by the diagonal base. (b)
and (c) are \Cref{thm:separable} read backwards; for (b), the second
fixed point of $T$ on the game $A_0$ of \Cref{prop:trans-stall} is
$\varphi=(\tfrac12,1,\tfrac34,1)$ at $(a,p,q,u)$, and
$\varphi(p)-\varphi(q)=\tfrac14$ is exactly the value at which
$\Delta^{T}$ stalls there.
\end{proof}

\Cref{lem:separable-lower} also supplies the tool that
\Cref{cor:separable}(c) asks for, and it is the analogue for
$\Delta^{T}$ of \Cref{thm:hybrid-convex-barrier}.

\begin{corollary}[Set certificates for the transitive
calculus]\label{cor:set-certificate}
Let $P_0\supseteq P_1\supseteq\cdots$ be nonempty subsets of $[0,1]^{V}$
and put $\Lambda(P)(x,y):=\sup_{z\in P}\bigl(z(x)-z(y)\bigr)$. If
$\Lambda(P_{k+1})$ is at most every clause of \Cref{def:slack}
evaluated at $\Lambda(P_{k})$, then $\Delta^{T}_k\ge\Lambda(P_k)$ for
every $k$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
$\Lambda(P)(x,y)\le\Lambda(P)(x,w)+\Lambda(P)(w,y)$ for every $w$,
because a single $z$ attaining the left side is admissible in both terms
on the right; and $\Lambda(P_0)\le1$ since $P_0\subseteq[0,1]^{V}$. So
$(\Lambda(P_k))_k$ is admissible in the sense of \Cref{def:separable}
and \Cref{lem:separable-lower} applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:set-certificate}
Like \Cref{thm:hybrid-convex-barrier}, this is a method and not a
barrier: its hypothesis is an invariant the prover has to supply, so by
itself it excludes no algorithm. What \Cref{thm:separable} adds is that
the method is the \emph{only} one available --- a certificate of the form
$\varphi(x)-\psi(y)$ cannot be made to work, and every $\Lambda(P)$ has
vanishing diagonal, which is precisely the feature the separable
certificates lack. The hypothesis is also genuinely restrictive: taking
$P_k:=\{z:l_k\le z\le u_k\}$ gives $\Lambda(P_k)(x,y)=u_k(x)-l_k(y)$
off the diagonal, which satisfies the clause conditions on the separated
configuration of \Cref{thm:slack-barrier} and, by
\Cref{prop:trans-Hm}, cannot satisfy them in general.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:trans}
Three corrections to the reading of \Cref{sec:slack} follow.
First, \Cref{thm:slack-barrier} and \Cref{cor:slack-stalls} are sharp for
the clauses they quantify over and for no others:
\Cref{prop:trans-Hm} turns the same family from $2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds
into $4m-3$. The barrier's proof cannot be repaired, and
\Cref{thm:separable} says why: an invariant a min-plus closure
propagates must satisfy the triangle inequality, which forces a
separable invariant $\varphi(x)-\psi(y)$ to have $\varphi=\psi$ and
hence to be a fixed point of $T$, of which a stopping game has exactly
one. The two-sided bound $u_k(x)-l_k(y)$ does satisfy the triangle
inequality --- an earlier version of this remark said otherwise --- but
its diagonal $u_k(z)-l_k(z)$ is strictly positive, which is what puts it
above $\Delta_k(z,z)\le0$ and confines \Cref{thm:slack-barrier} to two
disjoint parts.
Second, by \Cref{thm:trans-complete} no stall on a stopping game is ever
permanent: the mechanism of \Cref{sec:slack} expresses $w^\ast$ exactly in
the limit, and the entire question is how many rounds it needs. This is
the sense in which the frontier is a \emph{rate} question and not an
expressiveness question, and it is why \Cref{rem:magnitude}'s moral is
about magnitudes rather than about what can be said.
Third, \Cref{prop:trans-stall} shows that permanent blindness exists,
and \Cref{lem:phi-certificate} shows where it comes from: a second fixed
point of $T$ in $[0,1]^{V}$ that the calculus cannot tell from $w^\ast$.
On a stopping game there is no second fixed point (\Cref{thm:contraction})
and the calculus converges (\Cref{thm:trans-complete}); whether a
non-stopping game whose $T$ has a unique fixed point can be permanently
blind we have not determined --- and \Cref{thm:stopping-transform} sends
every instance into the stopping class. What is still not proved, and is what a polynomial
algorithm would need, is a polynomial bound on the number of rounds of
$\Delta^{T}$ on stopping games; by \Cref{thm:decide-one-bit} that is
again the whole problem.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
The moral is worth stating carefully, because it is not the one the
construction suggests. Precision is \emph{not} the obstacle: by
\Cref{thm:slack-sound} the slacks after $k$ rounds need only $O(k)$
bits, so a polynomial number of rounds is genuinely cheap. The obstacle
is that once arithmetic is admitted in this form, the mechanism cannot
outrun value iteration on parts of the game that do not interact, and
value iteration is exponential already with no players at all
(\Cref{thm:vi-lower}). \Cref{sec:simorder} and this section therefore
bracket the difficulty from both sides: a purely order-theoretic global
mechanism is cheap, beats every bounded radius, and fails on branch
compensation; the natural arithmetic repair fixes that and immediately
inherits the value-iteration lower bound. What escapes
\Cref{thm:slack-barrier} is a clause relating $x$ and $y$ through a third
vertex --- transitivity, $\Delta(x,y)\le\Delta(x,z)+\Delta(z,y)$, whose
premises leave the separated region and which is therefore not covered by
the theorem. Whether transitivity suffices is open.
\end{remark}

\subsection{A multiplicative calculus, and why it stops in the same place}
\label{sec:ratio}

Every mechanism above is additive: it propagates bounds on differences
$w^\ast(x)-w^\ast(y)$. The natural alternative is to propagate bounds on
\emph{ratios}. Two things make that worth doing. The first is that the
Shapley operator becomes positively homogeneous after one homogenisation
step, so the classical nonlinear Perron--Frobenius apparatus --- the
Collatz--Wielandt bounds, Hilbert's projective metric, Birkhoff's
contraction theorem --- becomes formally available; we show first that it
is not useful here. The second is that a ratio calculus is genuinely
\emph{not} an instance of \Cref{def:slack}, because at an average vertex
it uses the harmonic mean and the mediant where the additive calculus
uses the arithmetic mean. It therefore tests whether
\Cref{thm:slack-barrier} is an artefact of additivity. It is not.

\begin{definition}[Homogenisation]\label{def:homog}
Let $V'$ be the non-sinks of an SSG $G$ and let
$K:=\mathbb{R}_{\ge0}^{V'\cup\{\ast\}}$. For $y\in K$ define
$\hat y$ on $V$ by $\hat y(t_0):=0$, $\hat y(t_1):=y(\ast)$ and
$\hat y(v):=y(v)$ on $V'$, and put
\[
  (\widehat Ty)(v):=\mathrm{ag}_v\bigl(\hat y(v^{(0)}),\hat y(v^{(1)})\bigr)
  \ \ (v\in V'),\qquad (\widehat Ty)(\ast):=y(\ast),
\]
$\mathrm{ag}_v$ being $\max$, $\min$ or the mean according to the type of
$v$. Then $\widehat T$ is order-preserving and positively homogeneous of
degree one, and $x\mapsto(x,1)$ is a bijection between the fixed points of
$T$ in $[0,1]^{V}$ and the fixed points of $\widehat T$ with
$y(\ast)=1$ and $0\le y\le\bbone$. The box cannot be dropped: on the
non-stopping one-vertex game $c\to(c,c)$ every $y$ with $y(c)\ge0$ and
$y(\ast)=1$ is fixed by $\widehat T$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[The multiplicative eigenvalue is constant, and its
bracket bounds nothing]\label{prop:cw}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item For every SSG and every strictly positive $y\in K$,
\[
  \min_{u}\frac{(\widehat Ty)(u)}{y(u)}\ \le\ 1\ \le\
  \max_{u}\frac{(\widehat Ty)(u)}{y(u)} ,
\]
the ratio at $u=\ast$ being exactly $1$. Hence the cone spectral radius
of $\widehat T$ equals $1$ on every instance; no $y>0$ satisfies
$\widehat Ty\le\lambda y$ with $\lambda<1$ or $\widehat Ty\ge\lambda y$
with $\lambda>1$; and a bisection on $\lambda$ returns $1$ whatever the
game.
\item The Collatz--Wielandt bracket carries no information about
$\val$. For $L\ge2$ let $D_L$ be the stopping SSG whose non-sinks are
average vertices $c_1,\dots,c_L$ with $c_j\to(c_{j+1},c_{j+1})$ for
$j<L$ and $c_L\to(t_1,t_1)$, so $N=L+2$ and $w^\ast\equiv1$ on $V'$.
Then $y(c_j):=2^{-(L-j)}$, $y(\ast):=1$ satisfies
$1\le(\widehat Ty)(u)/y(u)\le2$ for every $u$, yet
$\max_{v}w^\ast(v)/y(v)=2^{L-1}=2^{N-3}$. So no function $c(\beta)$
makes ``$\widehat Ty\le\beta y$ implies $w^\ast\le c(\beta)y$'' true.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(a) $(\widehat Ty)(\ast)=y(\ast)$ exactly, so the ratio at $\ast$ is $1$
and lies between the minimum and the maximum; and $\widehat Ty\le\lambda y$
forces $y(\ast)\le\lambda y(\ast)$, that is $\lambda\ge1$, dually in the
other direction.
(b) $D_L$ is stopping because $c_1\to\cdots\to c_L\to t_1$ absorbs, and
$w^\ast\equiv1$ because every play reaches $t_1$. For $j<L$,
$(\widehat Ty)(c_j)=y(c_{j+1})=2y(c_j)$, and
$(\widehat Ty)(c_L)=\hat y(t_1)=y(\ast)=1=y(c_L)$; so the bracket is
$[1,2]$ while $w^\ast(c_1)/y(c_1)=2^{L-1}$. The multiplicative slack
cannot be scaled away, because rescaling $y$ on $V'$ does not rescale
$\hat y(t_1)=y(\ast)$: the sink pins the scale.
\end{proof}

What survives is not the eigenvalue but the calculus.

\begin{definition}[Ratio calculus]\label{def:ratio}
Let $G$ be an SSG and $Z_0,Z_1$ as in \Cref{def:simorder}. Entries lie in
$[0,+\infty]$ and are read as ``$w^\ast(x)\le R(x,y)\,w^\ast(y)$''. Put
$R_0\equiv+\infty$ and let $R_{k+1}(x,y)$ be the minimum of $R_k(x,y)$
with every applicable entry among: $0$ if $x\in Z_0$; $1$ if $y\in Z_1$;
$1$ if $x=y$;
\[
  \mathrm{ah}^{-}_{y}\bigl(R_k(x,y^{(0)}),R_k(x,y^{(1)})\bigr)
  \quad(y\text{ a non-sink}),\qquad
  \mathrm{ah}^{+}_{x}\bigl(R_k(x^{(0)},y),R_k(x^{(1)},y)\bigr)
  \quad(x\text{ a non-sink}),
\]
where $\mathrm{ah}^{-}_{y}$ is $\min$ at $\Vmax$, $\max$ at $\Vmin$ and
the \emph{harmonic} mean at $\Vavg$, while $\mathrm{ah}^{+}_{x}$ is
$\max$ at $\Vmax$, $\min$ at $\Vmin$ and the \emph{arithmetic} mean at
$\Vavg$; and, for $x,y$ non-sinks of the same type, the composites
$\min_j\max_i R_k(x^{(i)},y^{(j)})$ and $\max_i\min_j R_k(x^{(i)},y^{(j)})$
at two Max vertices, $\min_i\max_j$ and $\max_j\min_i$ at two Min
vertices, and $\max_i R_k\bigl(x^{(i)},y^{(\pi(i))}\bigr)$ for either
bijection $\pi$ at two average vertices. We use $c/0=+\infty$ for $c>0$
and read the harmonic mean of $0$ with anything as $0$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Soundness]\label{thm:ratio-sound}
For every SSG, every $k$ and all $x,y$: $w^\ast(x)\le R_k(x,y)w^\ast(y)$,
and $R_{k+1}\le R_k$. No stopping hypothesis is needed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $D(x,y):=w^\ast(x)/w^\ast(y)$, with the conventions above. It
suffices that every clause is at least $D$ when evaluated at a matrix
that is at least $D$; all clauses are monotone and $R_0=+\infty\ge D$.
Throughout, $Tw^\ast=w^\ast$ by \Cref{thm:lfp-general}.

Denominator clauses. At $y\in\Vmax$, $w^\ast(y)\ge w^\ast(y^{(j)})$ for
both $j$, so $w^\ast(x)\le R(x,y^{(j)})w^\ast(y^{(j)})\le
R(x,y^{(j)})w^\ast(y)$ for both, whence the $\min$. At $y\in\Vmin$ the
minimum is attained at some $j^\ast$ with $w^\ast(y)=w^\ast(y^{(j^\ast)})$,
so $w^\ast(x)\le R(x,y^{(j^\ast)})w^\ast(y)\le\max_jR(x,y^{(j)})w^\ast(y)$.
At $y\in\Vavg$, from $w^\ast(y^{(j)})\ge w^\ast(x)/R(x,y^{(j)})$,
\[
  w^\ast(y)=\tfrac12\bigl(w^\ast(y^{(0)})+w^\ast(y^{(1)})\bigr)
  \ \ge\ \tfrac{w^\ast(x)}2\Bigl(\tfrac1{R(x,y^{(0)})}+\tfrac1{R(x,y^{(1)})}\Bigr),
\]
which is the harmonic mean bound.

Numerator clauses. At $x\in\Vmax$, $w^\ast(x)=\max_iw^\ast(x^{(i)})\le
\max_iR(x^{(i)},y)\,w^\ast(y)$; at $x\in\Vmin$, $w^\ast(x)\le w^\ast(x^{(i)})
\le R(x^{(i)},y)w^\ast(y)$ for each $i$, whence the $\min$; at
$x\in\Vavg$ the arithmetic mean is immediate.

Composites. Write $a_i:=w^\ast(x^{(i)})$, $b_j:=w^\ast(y^{(j)})$. At two
Max vertices $D(x,y)=\max_ia_i/\max_jb_j$, which equals
$\max_i\min_j(a_i/b_j)$ and is at most $\min_j\max_i(a_i/b_j)$; at two
Min vertices $D(x,y)=\min_ia_i/\min_jb_j$ equals both
$\min_i\max_j(a_i/b_j)$ and $\max_j\min_i(a_i/b_j)$. At two average
vertices $D(x,y)=(a_0+a_1)/(b_{\pi(0)}+b_{\pi(1)})\le\max_i a_i/b_{\pi(i)}$
by the mediant inequality. The bases are immediate from
$Z_0\subseteq\{w^\ast=0\}$ and $Z_1\subseteq\{w^\ast=1\}$, and
$R_1\le R_0$ propagates to $R_{k+1}\le R_k$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The ratio calculus is sandwiched by value
iteration]\label{thm:ratio-sandwich}
Put $u_k:=T^k\bbone$ and $l_k:=T^k0$, the sinks not pinned in the initial
vectors.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item \textup{(Upper, no hypothesis.)} For every SSG, every $k$ and all
      $x,y$ including sinks, $R_{2k}(x,y)\le u_k(x)/l_k(y)$.
\item \textup{(Lower, the barrier.)} If $G$ is stopping and $A,B$ satisfy
      the hypotheses of \Cref{thm:slack-barrier} --- disjoint, each closed
      under non-sink successors, and $A\cup B$ disjoint from
      $Z_0\cup Z_1$ --- then $R_k(x,y)\ge u_k(x)/l_k(y)$ for every $k$ and
      every $(x,y)\in(A\cup\{t_0,t_1\})\times(B\cup\{t_0,t_1\})$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(a) is the argument of \Cref{thm:slack-vi-upper} in multiplicative form:
one round applies the numerator clause at $x$ and the next the
denominator clause at $y$; the arithmetic mean at an average numerator
reproduces $T$ in the numerator and the harmonic mean at an average
denominator reproduces $T$ in the denominator, while $\max$ against
$\min$ pairs as before, and $R_{k+1}\le R_k$ supplies the monotonicity.

(b) Put $E_k(x,y):=u_k(x)/l_k(y)$, so $E_0=+\infty=R_0$, and note that
$E_k$ is nonincreasing in $k$ because $u_k$ decreases and $l_k$
increases. The region $(A\cup\{t_0,t_1\})\times(B\cup\{t_0,t_1\})$ is
closed under passing to successors on either side, by the
successor-closedness of $A$ and $B$, and every clause is monotone; so it
is enough to check that each clause evaluated at $E_k$ is at least
$E_{k+1}$. The three denominator clauses all evaluate to exactly
$u_k(x)/l_{k+1}(y)$ --- $\min$ at Max against $\max$ inside $l$, $\max$
at Min against $\min$, and at an average $y$
\[
  \frac{2}{\dfrac{l_k(y^{(0)})}{u_k(x)}+\dfrac{l_k(y^{(1)})}{u_k(x)}}
  \ \text{ replaced by }\
  \frac{2u_k(x)}{l_k(y^{(0)})+l_k(y^{(1)})}=\frac{u_k(x)}{l_{k+1}(y)},
\]
the harmonic mean of the ratios being the ratio to the arithmetic mean of
the denominators --- and $u_{k+1}\le u_k$ gives
$u_k(x)/l_{k+1}(y)\ge E_{k+1}$. The three numerator clauses evaluate to
$u_{k+1}(x)/l_k(y)\ge E_{k+1}$ because $l_{k+1}\ge l_k$. The two Max and
two Min composites evaluate to exactly $E_{k+1}$, and the average
composite is at least $E_{k+1}$ by the mediant inequality. The
hypothesis $A\cup B$ disjoint from $Z_0\cup Z_1$ makes the two
$Z$-clauses applicable only at the sinks: at $x=t_0$ one has
$u_{k+1}(t_0)=0$ so the clause value $0$ equals $E_{k+1}$, and at
$y=t_1$ one has $l_{k+1}(t_1)=1$ so the clause value $1$ dominates
$u_{k+1}(x)\le1$. The diagonal clause is applicable only at
$x=y\in\{t_0,t_1\}$, because $A$ and $B$ are disjoint, and there
$E_{k+1}$ is $0/0=0$ and $1/1=1$ respectively. This last point is not a
technicality: off the sinks $E_k(x,x)=u_k(x)/l_k(x)>1$ exceeds the
diagonal clause, which is exactly why the two parts must be disjoint,
as in \Cref{cor:separable}(a).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Exact in the limit]\label{cor:ratio-complete}
Let $G$ be stopping and $M:=\lim_kR_k$, which exists by monotonicity and
\Cref{thm:ratio-sound}. Then $M(x,y)=w^\ast(x)/w^\ast(y)$ whenever
$w^\ast(y)>0$. Every stall of the ratio calculus is therefore a question
of the number of rounds, never of expressive power.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
$M\ge D$ is \Cref{thm:ratio-sound}, and
$M\le R_{2k}\le u_k(x)/l_k(y)\to w^\ast(x)/w^\ast(y)$ by
\Cref{thm:ratio-sandwich}(a) and \Cref{thm:contraction}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The same exponential stall]\label{cor:ratio-stall}
On the family $H_m$ of \Cref{cor:slack-stalls} the ratio calculus stalls
for $2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds. Taking $A=\{h\}$ and $B=\{c_1,\dots,c_{2m}\}$
in \Cref{thm:ratio-sandwich}(b) gives
$R_k(h,c_1)\ge\tfrac12/l_k(c_1)>1$ and
$R_k(c_1,h)\ge u_k(c_1)/\tfrac12\ge w^\ast(c_1)/\tfrac12>1$ for every
$k$ with $2\le k<\ln2\cdot2^{m-2}$ (the iterates start from the unpinned
constants, so $u_k(h)=l_k(h)=\tfrac12$ only from $k=2$ on), so neither
entry decides the pair and the multiplicative pair test abstains at the
unique controlled vertex; as in \Cref{cor:slack-stalls}, the
own-successor entries are not covered by the barrier. Measured exactly,
the own-successor test of \Cref{rem:own-successor} first fires at rounds
$15,39,101$ for $m=3,4,5$ --- the same rounds as the additive calculus
--- and the bound of \Cref{thm:ratio-sandwich}(b) is attained with
equality entry by entry: over the two entries $(h,c_1)$ and $(c_1,h)$ and
the rounds $k=1,\dots,K$ with $K=18,42,105$, equality holds at $32$ of the
$36$, $79$ of the $84$ and $204$ of the $210$ entries, the remainder being
the rounds at which $l_k(c_1)=0$, where the bound itself is $+\infty$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proposition}[Incomparable with the additive
calculus]\label{prop:ratio-incomparable}
Neither of \Cref{def:slack} and \Cref{def:ratio} dominates the other.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item On the stopping player-free game $W$ with average vertices $0,1,2$,
      $t_0=3$, $t_1=4$ and $0\to(t_0,2)$, $1\to(t_0,t_1)$, $2\to(t_1,1)$,
      one has $w^\ast=(\tfrac38,\tfrac12,\tfrac34)$ and
      $R_2(0,2)=\tfrac12<1$, which certifies $w^\ast(0)<w^\ast(2)$, while
      $\Delta_2(0,2)=0$ does not.
\item On the stopping player-free game $W'$ with seven average vertices,
      $t_0=7$, $t_1=8$ and out-edges
      $(6,5),(0,7),(1,4),(3,0),(8,5),(2,7),(5,4)$, one has
      $w^\ast=(\tfrac27,\tfrac17,\tfrac{18}{49},\tfrac27,\tfrac{29}{49},
      \tfrac9{49},\tfrac{19}{49})$ and $\Delta_7(3,6)=-\tfrac1{64}$
      certifies $w^\ast(3)<w^\ast(6)$, while $R_7(3,6)=\tfrac{259}{240}>1$
      does not; the ratio calculus catches up only at round $8$, with
      $\tfrac{221}{240}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Both games are stopping and player-free, so the stated value vectors are
the unique solutions of the corresponding linear systems, and all the
quoted entries are finite exact rational computations from
\Cref{def:slack} and \Cref{def:ratio}. The successive values are
$R_k(0,2)=\infty,\tfrac12,\tfrac12,\tfrac12$ against
$\Delta_k(0,2)=1,0,-\tfrac14,-\tfrac38$ in (a), and
$R_k(3,6)=\infty,\infty,4,\tfrac52,\tfrac74,\tfrac{53}{40},
\tfrac{259}{240},\tfrac{221}{240}$ against
$\Delta_k(3,6)=1,1,\tfrac12,\tfrac14,\tfrac18,\tfrac1{32},
-\tfrac1{64},-\tfrac3{64}$ in (b).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:ratio}
The point of this subsection is the combination of
\Cref{prop:ratio-incomparable} with \Cref{thm:ratio-sandwich}(b). The
ratio calculus is not an instance of \Cref{def:slack}: at an average
vertex it uses the harmonic mean on the denominator side and the mediant
in the composite, neither of which is an additive clause in disguise, and
on explicit instances it decides pairs the additive calculus cannot and
fails on pairs the additive calculus decides. Yet on a separated
configuration it obeys the \emph{same} two-sided value-iteration bound,
by the same $A,B$ and the same quantity $u_k,l_k$, and it stalls on the
same family $H_m$ for the same number of rounds. So
\Cref{thm:slack-barrier} is not a fact about additive propagation: what
it constrains is any calculus whose clauses match the two branches of an
average vertex one at a time, whatever the algebra used to combine them.
That is the same moral as \Cref{rem:magnitude}, reached from the other
side.

Two limits. \Cref{prop:cw} closes the multiplicative \emph{eigenvalue}
route but says nothing about the calculus; and
\Cref{cor:ratio-complete} means the ratio calculus, like
\Cref{def:trans-slack}, can only ever be defeated by a rate, so
\Cref{cor:ratio-stall} is a statement about a particular family and not
about the mechanism in general. We do not know whether a min-plus ---
here min-times --- closure of \Cref{def:ratio} escapes
\Cref{thm:ratio-sandwich}(b) as \Cref{prop:trans-Hm} escapes
\Cref{thm:slack-barrier}.
\end{remark}


The two calculi are the endpoints of a one-parameter family, and the
barrier holds along all of it for a reason that does not involve the
parameter.

\begin{definition}[The M\"obius family]\label{def:mobius}
For $\beta\ge0$ let $G^{\beta}$ be $G$ with the sinks paid $\beta$ and
$1+\beta$ instead of $0$ and $1$. Since $x\mapsto x+\beta$ is an
increasing affine bijection and $\max$, $\min$ and the mean all commute
with it, the operator of $G^\beta$ is
$T^{\beta}(x)=\beta+T(x-\beta)$; consequently $G^\beta$ has value
$w^\ast+\beta$ and its value iterates from the constants $1+\beta$ and
$\beta$ are $u_k+\beta$ and $l_k+\beta$. Let $R^{\beta}$ be
\Cref{def:ratio} run on $G^{\beta}$: entries read
\[
  w^\ast(x)+\beta\ \le\ R^{\beta}(x,y)\,\bigl(w^\ast(y)+\beta\bigr),
\]
the clauses are those of \Cref{def:ratio} verbatim, and for $\beta>0$ the
bases become $1$ if $x\in Z_0$, $1$ if $y\in Z_1$, $\beta/(1+\beta)$ if
both, $1$ if $x=y$, together with the global cap $(1+\beta)/\beta$ in
place of $R_0\equiv+\infty$. Thus $R^{0}=R$ is \Cref{def:ratio}.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[The whole family stops in the same
place]\label{prop:mobius}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item For every $\beta\ge0$ and every SSG, $R^{\beta}$ is sound in the
      above reading and $R^{\beta}_{k+1}\le R^{\beta}_k$.
\item \Cref{thm:ratio-sandwich} holds verbatim for $R^{\beta}$ with
      $u_k,l_k$ replaced by $u_k+\beta$ and $l_k+\beta$: the upper bound
      $R^{\beta}_{2k}(x,y)\le(u_k(x)+\beta)/(l_k(y)+\beta)$ with no
      hypothesis, and the lower bound
      $R^{\beta}_{k}(x,y)\ge(u_k(x)+\beta)/(l_k(y)+\beta)$ on the
      separated configurations of \Cref{thm:slack-barrier}.
\item The decisive condition does not involve $\beta$:
      $(u_k(x)+\beta)/(l_k(y)+\beta)>1$ if and only if $u_k(x)>l_k(y)$.
      Hence \emph{every} member of the family stalls on $H_m$ for
      $2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds, and the first firing round of the
      own-successor test is the same for all of them.
\item The family is nevertheless not a reparametrisation: its members
      do not decide pairs in a fixed order. On the game $W$ of
      \Cref{prop:ratio-incomparable}(a) the pair $(0,2)$ is decided at
      round $2$ by $\beta=0$ and only at round $3$ by
      $\beta=\tfrac14,1,4$; on $W'$ the pair $(3,6)$ is decided at round
      $7$ by $\beta=1,4$ and only at round $8$ by $\beta=0,\tfrac14$. These
      two instances separate $\beta=0$ from $\beta\in\{1,4\}$ in both
      directions and $\beta=\tfrac14$ from $\beta=0$ in one; we have not
      separated $\beta=1$ from $\beta=4$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(a) and (b) are \Cref{thm:ratio-sound} and \Cref{thm:ratio-sandwich}
applied to $G^{\beta}$. Those proofs use only that $T$ fixes the value
vector, that the three aggregators are $\max$, $\min$ and the mean, and
that the extreme values are attained on $Z_0$ and $Z_1$; all four survive
the substitution of $\beta,1+\beta$ for the sink payoffs, and the value
iterates transform as stated in \Cref{def:mobius}. For $\beta>0$ every
value is at least $\beta>0$, so no ratio is ever $0/0$ and the entries
are finite from the start. (c) is immediate from
$(c+\beta)/(t+\beta)>1\iff c>t$, and $H_m$ then follows exactly as in
\Cref{cor:ratio-stall}. (d) is a finite exact computation.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:mobius}
The family interpolates. Writing $R^{\beta}=1+\Delta/\beta$ and expanding
as $\beta\to\infty$, the harmonic mean of $1+a/\beta$ and $1+b/\beta$ is
$1+\tfrac12(a+b)/\beta+O(\beta^{-2})$, the arithmetic mean is the same to
first order, and $\min$, $\max$ and the composites transform into their
additive counterparts; so the clause set of $R^{\beta}$ converges to that
of \Cref{def:slack}, of which \Cref{def:ratio} is the opposite end at
$\beta=0$. The measurements agree: on $W'$ the additive calculus decides
$(3,6)$ at round $7$, and so do $R^{1}$ and $R^{4}$, while $R^{0}$ needs
round $8$.

What this adds to \Cref{rem:ratio} is that the obstruction is not located
at either endpoint. A continuum of mutually incomparable sound
mechanisms, differing in which pairs they decide and in what order, is
stopped by one family at one round count, because the quantity that
decides whether the barrier bound is informative --- the sign of
$u_k(x)-l_k(y)$ --- is invariant along the whole family. Escaping it
requires leaving the family altogether, in the direction
\Cref{def:trans-slack} takes: a closure clause whose premises leave the
separated region. That direction does work here as well.

\Cref{thm:separable} transfers to that question unchanged, for the
members of \Cref{def:mobius} with $\beta>0$ (at $\beta=0$ the base
clause $M(t_0,y)\le0$ admits no positive matrix at all, so the hypothesis
below is vacuous there). Suppose a sequence
$M_k(x,y)=\varphi_k(x)/\psi_k(y)$ of positive matrices is dominated by
every clause of the calculus at $M_k$ and closed under the min-times
triangle inequality
$M(x,y)\le M(x,z)M(z,y)$. The latter reads
$1\le\varphi_k(z)/\psi_k(z)$, so $\psi_k\le\varphi_k$, while the
diagonal base $M_k(x,x)\le1$ gives $\varphi_k\le\psi_k$; so
$\varphi_k=\psi_k$. Evaluated at $(x,x)$ the numerator clause is
$(T\varphi_k)(x)/\varphi_k(x)$ in all three cases, and the denominator
clause is $\varphi_k(x)/(T\varphi_k)(x)$ --- the harmonic mean of
$\varphi_k(x)/\varphi_k(x^{(j)})$ being $\varphi_k(x)$ over the
arithmetic mean of the $\varphi_k(x^{(j)})$ --- so both being at least
$1$ forces $T\varphi_k=\varphi_k$ at every non-sink, whence on a
stopping game $\varphi_k$ is the increasing affine image
$\alpha'+(\beta'-\alpha')w^\ast$ of the value vector determined by its
own sink values $\alpha'=\varphi_k(t_0)<\beta'=\varphi_k(t_1)$, by
\Cref{lem:gen-comparison} --- the strict inequality being supplied by the
base $M_k(t_0,t_1)\le\beta/(1+\beta)<1$. So here too a rate proof needs a certificate
that is not a ratio of two potentials.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[The multiplicative closure escapes, too]\label{prop:ratio-closure}
Let $R^{T}$ be \Cref{def:ratio} with each round followed by the
\emph{min-times} closure
$R(x,y)\le R(x,z)R(z,y)$, the all-pairs shortest-path closure of
\Cref{def:trans-slack} read multiplicatively. Then $R^{T}$ is sound on
every SSG, and it escapes \Cref{thm:ratio-sandwich}(b): on the family
$H_m$, on which $R$ itself stalls for $2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds by
\Cref{cor:ratio-stall}, the own-successor test fires at round
$7,11,15,20,24$ for $m=3,\dots,7$. As with \Cref{prop:wedge} we state
this as a measurement, verified in exact rational arithmetic, and not as
the linear bound it points to.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Soundness: with the conventions $c/0=+\infty$ for $c>0$, $0/0=0$ and
$(+\infty)\cdot0=+\infty$ (a product with an infinite factor is
infinite, so that a chain through a vertex of value $0$ bounds nothing),
one has $D(x,y)\le D(x,z)D(z,y)$ for the true ratios, with equality
whenever $w^\ast(z)>0$; the clause is monotone, so it may be added to the
induction of \Cref{thm:ratio-sound} unchanged. The escape is a finite
exact computation: measured for the variant in which every finite entry
is rounded up to a multiple of $2^{-(2a+2)}$ and every entry $R(x,y)$
with $y\notin Z_0$ is capped at $2^{a}$ --- rounding up is sound, and so
is the cap at those pairs, because a positive value is at least $2^{-a}$
by \Cref{lem:denominator-sharp}; at pairs with $y\in Z_0$ the true ratio
may be infinite and no cap is applied --- the first firing rounds are as
stated, against $9,13,17,21,25$ for \Cref{def:trans-slack} and
$15,39,101$ for $R$ and $\Delta$ at $m=3,4,5$. Rounding up can only
delay a firing, but the cap can only hasten one, so the counts are those
of the rounded and capped variant and are not claimed to bound the exact
iteration, which is impractical here because the min-times closure
squares denominators. Soundness was asserted against $w^\ast$ at every
entry and every round of every run.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:ratio-closure}
So the escape from the value-iteration sandwich is a property of the
\emph{closure clause} and not of the algebra used to combine branches:
\Cref{prop:trans-Hm} and \Cref{prop:ratio-closure} are the additive and
multiplicative halves of one phenomenon, and the multiplicative half is
marginally the faster of the two on this family. What
\Cref{thm:slack-barrier} and \Cref{thm:ratio-sandwich}(b) constrain is
exactly the clauses whose premises stay inside the separated region;
what neither constrains is a premise that leaves it. Both closures are
then covered by \Cref{thm:separable} and by the multiplicative form of
it above: whatever bounds their rate from below cannot be a difference,
or a ratio, of two potentials.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The branch-compensation barrier}\label{sec:matching-barrier}

The failures of \Cref{sec:simorder} can now be made into a theorem about
a whole class of mechanisms at once. The class abstracts what
\Cref{def:simorder} and every rule like it have in common: facts of the
form $w^\ast(x)\le w^\ast(y)$ are derived from such facts about the
\emph{successors} of $x$ and $y$, with no arithmetic on values.

\begin{definition}[Local matching calculus]\label{def:lmc}
The \emph{frame} of an ordered pair $(p,q)$ of vertices consists of the
six formal symbols $x,x^{(0)},x^{(1)},y,y^{(0)},y^{(1)}$, interpreted at
$p$, $p$'s successors, $q$ and $q$'s successors, together with their six
types (among $\max,\min,\mathrm{avg},t_0,t_1$) and the equality pattern
of their interpretations. An \emph{atom} is a pair $(l,r)$ of a left and
a right symbol other than $(x,y)$; a \emph{clause} is a set of atoms. A
\emph{local matching calculus} assigns to each frame a set of clauses,
and derives $(p,q)$ when some clause assigned to its frame has all its
atoms true --- an atom $(l,r)$ being true when the pair of
interpretations $(\llbracket l\rrbracket,\llbracket r\rrbracket)$ has
itself been derived --- on top of an unconditional base containing
$(Z_0\times V)\cup(V\times Z_1)$ and at most the diagonal in addition.
The derived relation may be read inductively (least fixed point) or
coinductively (greatest); the calculus is \emph{sound} if in every
stopping SSG every derived pair $(p,q)$ satisfies
$w^\ast(p)\le w^\ast(q)$. \Cref{def:simorder} is such a calculus.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Fooling partner]\label{lem:fooling-partner}
Let $G_{\mathrm{f}}$ be the stopping SSG whose non-sink vertices
$a_0,a_1,a_2,b_0,b_1,d,e$ are all average, with
\[
  a_0\to(b_0,a_1),\quad a_1\to(b_1,a_2),\quad a_2\to(t_0,t_0),\quad
  b_i\to(b_i,t_1),\quad d\to(e,t_0),\quad e\to(e,t_1).
\]
Then $w^\ast=(\tfrac34,\tfrac12,0,1,1,\tfrac12,1)$ in the listed order,
$Z_0=\{a_2,t_0\}$ and $Z_1=\{b_0,b_1,e,t_1\}$. Writing
$\Pi:=\{(x,y^{(0)}),(x^{(0)},y^{(0)}),(x^{(1)},y),(x^{(1)},y^{(0)})\}$:
the pair $(4,1)$ of $G_8$ (\Cref{prop:simorder-stalls}) and the pair
$(a_0,d)$ of $G_{\mathrm{f}}$ have the same frame, and in both games the set of
\emph{true} atoms --- those whose interpretations satisfy the value
inequality --- is exactly $\Pi$; yet
$w^\ast(4)=\tfrac25<\tfrac12=w^\ast(1)$ while
$w^\ast(a_0)=\tfrac34>\tfrac12=w^\ast(d)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
$G_{\mathrm{f}}$ is player-free and every vertex is absorbed almost surely, so
its values are the unique solution of the linear system:
$w^\ast(b_i)=\tfrac12(w^\ast(b_i)+1)=1$, likewise $w^\ast(e)=1$;
$w^\ast(a_2)=0$; $w^\ast(a_1)=\tfrac12(1+0)=\tfrac12$;
$w^\ast(a_0)=\tfrac12(1+\tfrac12)=\tfrac34$;
$w^\ast(d)=\tfrac12(1+0)=\tfrac12$. In $G_8$, $4\to(3,2)$ and
$1\to(0,t_0)$ give the interpretation types
$(\mathrm{avg},\mathrm{avg},\mathrm{avg}\mid\mathrm{avg},\mathrm{avg},t_0)$,
all six interpretations distinct; in $G_{\mathrm{f}}$, $a_0\to(b_0,a_1)$ and
$d\to(e,t_0)$ give the same. The value tuples are
$(\tfrac25,\tfrac35,\tfrac15;\tfrac12,1,0)$ and
$(\tfrac34,1,\tfrac12;\tfrac12,1,0)$, and checking the eight atoms in
each game leaves exactly $\Pi$ true both times.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Branch-compensation barrier]\label{thm:matching-barrier}
No sound local matching calculus derives $(4,1)$ in $G_8$, in either
reading. Since $(1,4)$ is excluded by soundness outright and $5$ is the
unique controlled vertex of $G_8$, with successors of distinct optimal
values, \emph{every} sound local matching calculus stalls on $G_8$; by
\Cref{thm:decide-one-bit}, no such calculus yields a polynomial
algorithm. The conclusion survives replacing the derived relation by its
transitive closure, and it covers the own-successor test of
\Cref{rem:own-successor}: the value-true pair $(5,1)$, whose derivation
would decide $5$, is excluded in the transitive-closure paragraph of the
proof.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Suppose a sound calculus derives $(4,1)$. The pair is not in the base
($4\notin Z_0$, $1\notin Z_1$, $4\ne1$), so some clause $A$ assigned to
its frame has all atoms derived, and soundness makes every derived pair
value-true; by \Cref{lem:fooling-partner} the value-true atoms at $(4,1)$
are exactly $\Pi$, so $A\subseteq\Pi$. Now run the same calculus on
$G_{\mathrm{f}}$. The pairs $(a_1,d)$ and $(a_0,d)$ both carry the frame of
$(4,1)$, so $A$ is assigned to both. At $(a_1,d)$ the atoms of $\Pi$
interpret as $(a_1,e),(b_1,e),(a_2,d),(a_2,e)$, each in the base (right
member in $Z_1$ or left member in $Z_0$); at $(a_0,d)$ they interpret as
$(a_0,e),(b_0,e),(a_1,d),(a_1,e)$, each in the base except $(a_1,d)$,
which has just been derived. The derivation is well-founded, so both
readings derive $(a_0,d)$ --- but
$w^\ast(a_0)=\tfrac34>\tfrac12=w^\ast(d)$, contradicting soundness.

For the transitive closure: every member of a chain from $4$ to $1$ has
value in $[\tfrac25,\tfrac12]$ by soundness, hence lies in $\{1,4,5\}$ by
inspection of the value multiset of $G_8$, so the last link into $1$ comes
from $4$ or from $5$ and a chain $4\preceq v\preceq1$ suffices; $v\in\{1,4\}$ reuses the pair
$(4,1)$ itself, so let $v=5$, and consider the link $(5,1)$. Its
value-true atoms are $\Pi\cup\{(x^{(0)},y)\}$, and $(x^{(0)},y)$
interprets as $(4,1)$, underivable by the above; so a clause deriving
$(5,1)$ is contained in $\Pi$. Let $K_{\#}$ be the stopping SSG with
average vertices $g\to(g,t_1)$, $h\to(h,t_1)$, $k\to(h,t_0)$ and Max
vertex $m\to(g,k)$, so $w^\ast=(1,1,\tfrac12,1)$. The pair $(m,k)$
carries the frame of $(5,1)$, equality pattern
$\llbracket x^{(1)}\rrbracket=\llbracket y\rrbracket$ included; the atoms
of $\Pi$ interpret at $(m,k)$ as $(m,h),(g,h),(k,k),(k,h)$, each in the
base or on the diagonal --- and the diagonal pair $(k,k)$ is derived even
when the base carries no diagonal: if the clause omits the atom
$(x^{(1)},y)$ its atoms at $(m,k)$ are $(m,h),(g,h),(k,h)$, all in
$V\times Z_1$; if it contains that atom, then $(1,1)$ is derived in $G_8$
by some clause assigned to $\mathrm{frame}(1,1)=\mathrm{frame}(k,k)$, both
frames having types $(\mathrm{avg},\mathrm{avg},t_0,\mathrm{avg},\mathrm{avg},t_0)$
and the equality pattern $\{x=y,\ x^{(0)}=y^{(0)},\ x^{(1)}=y^{(1)}\}$,
and that clause's value-true atoms interpret at $(k,k)$ as
$(k,h),(h,h),(t_0,k),(t_0,h),(t_0,t_0)$, all in the base --- so the clause
derives $(m,k)$ --- claiming
$1\le\tfrac12$. Hence no sound clause derives $(5,1)$ either, and the
chain through $5$ is broken. All the numbers above were re-verified in
exact rational arithmetic, ground truth by enumeration of positional
pairs.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:magnitude}
The mechanism of the fooling pair names the obstruction more precisely
than the remark after \Cref{prop:simorder-stalls} did. In both games the
right-hand vertex splits into a branch worth $1$ and a branch worth $0$,
and the one-to-one matching succeeds against the first and fails against
the second; what distinguishes the games is only \emph{how much} surplus
the good branch carries against \emph{how much} deficit the bad branch
costs --- $\tfrac25$ against $\tfrac15$ in $G_8$, $0$ against $\tfrac12$
in $G_{\mathrm{f}}$. A sound mechanism must therefore compare the
\emph{magnitudes} of a surplus and a deficit, which no order-only
premise over the radius-one frame can express. This does not say the
comparison requires numbers --- only that it requires more than the
order data the frame exposes; and \Cref{sec:slack} shows that the most
natural numerical repair, sound and cheap though it is, then fails for
the independent reason of \Cref{thm:slack-barrier}. The two barriers
frame the mechanism-design question exactly: enough expressiveness to
weigh surplus against deficit, without collapsing into value iteration
on non-interacting parts.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Seeding from policy evaluation}\label{sec:seeded}

The slack calculus was run above from the trivial start $\Delta_0\equiv1$.
It is sound from any correct start, and a correct start is cheap: a
\emph{bracket} $L\le w^\ast\le U$ obtained by evaluating a few strategies,
each evaluation one linear program (\Cref{thm:one-player,thm:eval-stopfree}).

\begin{definition}[Seeded slack calculus]\label{def:seeded}
Given $L,U\in\mathcal{X}$ with $L\le w^\ast\le U$, put
$\Delta^{L,U}_0(x,y):=\mathrm{clamp}_{[-1,1]}\bigl(U(x)-L(y)\bigr)$ and
iterate the clauses of \Cref{def:slack}, keeping the running minimum. The
associated decision rule reports the pair $(x,y)$ when
$\Delta^{L,U}_k(x,y)<0$. Write $M(p,k)$ for the rule that first performs
$p$ rounds of switch-all improvement to build $L$ (and the matching
greedy $\tau$ for $U$), then runs $k$ slack rounds.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Soundness, domination, cost]\label{thm:seeded-sound}
For every SSG and every pair $L\le w^\ast\le U$ as in
\Cref{def:seeded}: $\Delta^{L,U}_k\ge
w^\ast(x)-w^\ast(y)$ for all $k$; the sequence is nonincreasing; it is
monotone in the seed, so $\Delta^{L,U}_k\le\Delta^{0,1}_k$ pointwise and
the seeded calculus dominates \Cref{def:slack} round by round; it decides
at $k=0$ every pair that the bracket $(L,U)$ decides in the sense of
\Cref{def:bracket}; and if $L,U$ have common denominator $d$ then every
entry of $\Delta_k$ lies in $(2^kd)^{-1}\mathbb{Z}$, so $k$ rounds cost
$O(kN^{2})$ rational operations on $O(k+a)$-bit numbers. No stopping
hypothesis is used.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
$\Delta_0\ge D$ because $U\ge w^\ast\ge L$. Every clause is monotone and
maps $D$ to $D$ (\Cref{thm:slack-sound}, which uses only $Tw^\ast=w^\ast$
and hence \Cref{thm:lfp-general}), so the running minimum of quantities
$\ge D$ stays $\ge D$; clamping preserves this since $D\in[-1,1]$.
Monotonicity in the seed is monotonicity of the clauses and of $\min$.
The $k=0$ claim is the definition of a bracket deciding a pair. The
denominator statement is as in \Cref{thm:slack-sound}, and
\Cref{lem:denominator-sharp} bounds $d$ by $2^{a}$ for each evaluated
strategy.
\end{proof}

Seeding does \emph{not} evade \Cref{thm:slack-barrier}, and it is worth
being explicit that the barrier is a statement about the geometry of the
game rather than about the initialisation.

\begin{theorem}[The barrier lifts to every seed]\label{thm:seeded-barrier}
Let $G$ be stopping and $(L,U)$ a bracket with $L\le TL$ and $TU\le U$.
Put $u_k:=T^{k}U$ and $l_k:=T^{k}L$. Under the hypotheses of
\Cref{thm:slack-barrier}, $\Delta^{L,U}_k(x,y)\ge u_k(x)-l_k(y)$ for all
$k$ and all $(x,y)$ in the separated region.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Verbatim the proof of \Cref{thm:slack-barrier} with
$E_k(x,y):=u_k(x)-l_k(y)$. The base case is now an equality, since
$L,U\in\mathcal{X}$ makes the clamp inactive; and $TU\le U$, $L\le TL$
with monotonicity of $T$ give $u_{k+1}\le u_k$ and $l_{k+1}\ge l_k$,
which is exactly what the (down) and (up) steps use.
\end{proof}

What seeding does buy is that the separated geometry can no longer
produce a stall at all, because on separated parts the bracket is already
exact.

\begin{theorem}[Exactness dichotomy]\label{thm:seed-dichotomy}
Let $G$ be stopping. If $\sigma$ has no strictly switchable vertex and
$\tau$ is greedy for $\val_\sigma$ at every Min vertex, then
$\val_\sigma=\val^\tau=w^\ast$, so $\Delta_0=D$ and every controlled
vertex with distinct successor values is decided at slack round $0$.
Consequently, if switch-all improvement started from $\sigma_0$ reaches
$\Opt$ within $p$ rounds, or if $\Vmax=\emptyset$, then $M(p,0)$ decides
every such vertex. Contrapositively, every instance on which $M(p,k)$
stalls is an instance on which switch-all improvement has \emph{not}
converged after $p$ rounds. (The case $\Vmin=\emptyset$ is \emph{not}
covered: there $U=w^\ast$, but $L=\val_{\sigma_p}$ need not be, and
$M(0,0)$ can stall on a one-player game; see the end of the proof.)
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
$\val_\sigma=w^\ast$ is \Cref{lem:local-global}. For $v\in\Vmin$,
$w^\ast(\tau(v))=\min_j w^\ast(v^{(j)})=(Tw^\ast)(v)=w^\ast(v)$, and
$T_\tau$ agrees with $T$ elsewhere, so $T_\tau w^\ast=w^\ast$; freezing
$\tau$ leaves a stopping game with no Min vertices whose Shapley operator
is $T_\tau$, and \Cref{thm:contraction} gives uniqueness of its fixed
point, so $\val^\tau=w^\ast$. With $L=U=w^\ast$ we get $\Delta_0=D$, one
of whose two orders is negative at every distinguishing vertex. For
$\Vmax=\emptyset$ the profile $\sigma$ is vacuous, so $\val_\sigma=w^\ast$,
and the first part gives $\val^\tau=w^\ast$. For $\Vmin=\emptyset$ the
$O(N)$ fixed-point test $TU=U$ identifies $U$ with $w^\ast$ by
\Cref{thm:contraction}, but $L=\val_{\sigma_p}$ is identified with
nothing, and $\Delta_0=U-L$ can be positive at every entry the rule
reads: on $WD(4,2,6)$ of \Cref{def:wedge}, from the profile
$v_i\mapsto b_i$, one has $\val_{\sigma_0}(v_i)=\val_{\sigma_0}(b_i)=\tfrac7{16}$
and $\val_{\sigma_0}(a_{i,1})=\tfrac{113}{256}$ against
$w^\ast(v_i)=w^\ast(a_{i,1})=\tfrac12$ and $w^\ast(b_i)=\tfrac{31}{64}$,
so $\Delta_0(v_1,a_{1,1})=\tfrac{15}{256}$,
$\Delta_0(a_{1,1},v_1)=\tfrac1{16}$, $\Delta_0(v_1,b_1)=\tfrac1{16}$ and
$\Delta_0(b_1,v_1)=\tfrac3{64}$ are all positive (exact arithmetic), and
$M(0,0)$ decides neither Max vertex. A rule that set $L:=U$ once $TU=U$
had succeeded would not stall there; $M$ as defined does. (An earlier
version of this theorem claimed the one-player case for both players.)
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Behaviour on the stalling instances of this paper]\label{prop:seeded-decides}
The rule $M(p,k)$ decides, in exact rational arithmetic: the game $G_8$
of \Cref{prop:simorder-stalls} already at $M(0,0)$, since $\Vmin=\emptyset$
there; every controlled vertex of the game $G^{\ast}$ of
\Cref{prop:simorder-stalls}, at $M(0,k)$ for $k\ge17$ from the
all-first-successor profile (its Max vertices fire by $k=7$ and its Min
vertex $5$ at $k=17$; from other profiles all seven fire at $k=0$); the
family $H_m$ of \Cref{cor:slack-stalls}, at $M(0,0)$ from $\sigma(v)=h$, by
clause (i) at $v$ and by the pair entry $\Delta_0(h,c_1)=-2^{-(m+1)}$ ---
not because the bracket is exact there, which it is not, as
$\val_\sigma(v)=\tfrac12<w^\ast(v)$; and, on the $31$-vertex game of
\Cref{prop:pdc-separation} with the strategy $\sigma$ used there, the
Max vertex $0$, at $k=3$ with $\Delta_3(2,1)=-\tfrac18$ --- although
\Cref{prop:pdc-separation}(a) shows that \emph{no} sound upper bound
decides that vertex from its lower bound, which has $L(1)=\tfrac{11}{32}$
there; the $L=\val_\sigma$ used here has $L(1)=\tfrac5{16}$, smaller still.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Finite exact computations; $w^\ast$ in each case by enumeration of the
positional pairs. The last item is the informative one: the bracket test
$L(1)>U(2)$ fails, since $L(1)=\tfrac5{16}<\tfrac38=U(2)$, and by
\Cref{prop:bracket}(a) no sound $U$ repairs it; but the slack clauses do
not compare $L$ against $U$ coordinatewise, they propagate a difference,
and $\Delta_3(2,1)=-\tfrac18$ certifies $w^\ast(2)<w^\ast(1)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:frontier-moved}
Three mechanisms have now been measured against the same yardstick, and
the frontier has moved to a precise place. A purely order-theoretic
global calculus beats every bounded radius and dies on branch
compensation, unconditionally for its whole class
(\Cref{thm:matching-barrier}). Adding slacks repairs that and inherits
the value-iteration bound on non-interacting parts
(\Cref{thm:slack-barrier}), a limitation that seeding cannot remove
(\Cref{thm:seeded-barrier}) but that the exactness of an evaluated
bracket makes vacuous exactly there (\Cref{thm:seed-dichotomy}).
\Cref{prop:trans-Hm} then shows that the geometric limitation is not
even sharp for the mechanism, only for its clause set: one extra clause,
whose premises leave the separated region, collapses the family the
barrier was built on. And by \Cref{thm:trans-complete} the whole family
of calculi is complete in the limit on stopping games, so what is at
stake is a rate and never an expressive power.
The residue is sharp: by \Cref{thm:seed-dichotomy} a stalling instance of
$M(p,k)$ for polynomial $p$ is in particular a stopping SSG on which
switch-all improvement runs for more than $p$ rounds, so exhibiting one
would settle a question \Cref{thm:ladder} leaves open --- there the
exponential run belongs to the least-index rule, and switch-all finishes
in $n$ steps. No superpolynomial lower bound for switch-all is proved in
this document, and none is claimed.
\end{remark}








\begin{remark}
The converse direction is of course not a route to an algorithm --- it
consumes the very oracle one is trying to build. Its content is
negative and precise: \textsc{(Poly-Rule)} is neither weaker nor
stronger than \Cref{prob:main}, so no partial progress on switching rules
can be worth less, or more, than the whole problem. This is why
\Cref{def:missing} is reported as a gap rather than as a plan.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Why the results above do not supply \textsc{(Poly-Rule)}]
\Cref{thm:short-path} guarantees that a path of length at most $n$
exists from every $\sigma$ to $\Opt$, but its proof is
non-constructive in the decisive respect: it selects a disagreement
vertex with a fixed but \emph{unknown} optimal $\sigma^\ast$, and
knowledge of such a $\sigma^\ast$ is precisely knowledge of the answer.
\Cref{thm:all-switches-refuted} rules out the all-switches rule as a
dominating choice, and \Cref{thm:cyclic-uso} rules out potential-based
arguments on the two-player cube. What survives from the analysis is a
guarantee of a different shape: the random-facet rule admits the proved
expected bound $e^{2\sqrt n}$ of \Cref{thm:subexp}, which is
subexponential but not polynomial, and which is tight for that algorithm.
Closing the gap therefore requires a genuinely new mechanism for
\emph{selecting} switches, not a sharper analysis of the rules considered
here.
\end{remark}

\subsection{A linear-programming certificate, and a complementary
barrier}\label{sec:transport}

The three mechanisms above propagate a relation or a slack from vertex to
vertex, one clause at a time. The fourth does not propagate at all: it
writes down the monotonicity that $Tw^\ast=w^\ast$ imposes as a system of
linear inequalities and optimises over it. It is polynomial, it decides
both instances on which the previous mechanisms were shown to fail, and
it fails elsewhere, for a reason exactly complementary to
\Cref{thm:slack-barrier}.

\begin{definition}[Transport polytope and separator]\label{def:transport}
For an SSG $G$ and vectors $L\le U$ in $\mathcal X$ let
\[
  Q(G;L,U):=\Bigl\{x\in\mathcal X:\ L\le x\le U,\
  \begin{aligned}
   &x(v)\ge x(v^{(i)})\ (v\in\Vmax,\ i=0,1),\\
   &x(v)\le x(v^{(i)})\ (v\in\Vmin,\ i=0,1),\\
   &x(v)=\tfrac12\bigl(x(v^{(0)})+x(v^{(1)})\bigr)\ (v\in\Vavg)
  \end{aligned}\Bigr\},
\]
with $x(t_0)=0$, $x(t_1)=1$. Write $Q(G):=Q(G;0,1)$ and
\[
  \operatorname{Sep}(p,q):=\max\{x(q)-x(p):x\in Q(G;L,U)\}.
\]
This is a linear program with $N$ variables and $O(N)$ rows, every row
read directly off the game.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Soundness]\label{thm:transport-sound}
For every SSG and all $L\le w^\ast\le U$ we have $w^\ast\in Q(G;L,U)$;
consequently $\operatorname{Sep}(p,q)\le0$ certifies
$w^\ast(q)\le w^\ast(p)$, and $\operatorname{Sep}(p,q)\ge w^\ast(q)-w^\ast(p)$.
No stopping hypothesis is needed, and the certificate is computable in
polynomial time.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
$Tw^\ast=w^\ast$ (\Cref{thm:lfp-general}) says $w^\ast(v)$ is the
maximum, the minimum or the mean of $w^\ast$ over the two successors,
according to the type of $v$; the first gives
$w^\ast(v)\ge w^\ast(v^{(i)})$ for both $i$, the second the reverse, the
third the equality. So $w^\ast\in Q(G)$, and it lies in $Q(G;L,U)$
whenever $L\le w^\ast\le U$. Everything else is immediate.
\end{proof}

The strength of the certificate comes from a single observation: the
average rows are \emph{equalities}, so wherever they close up they pin
$x$ exactly.

\begin{lemma}[Elimination]\label{lem:transport-exact}
Let $G$ be stopping and let $A\subseteq\Vavg$ be closed under successors
in the sense that every successor of a vertex of $A$ lies in
$A\cup\{t_0,t_1\}$. Then every $x\in Q(G)$ satisfies $x=w^\ast$ on $A$,
so $\operatorname{Sep}(p,q)=w^\ast(q)-w^\ast(p)$ for $p,q\in A$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
On $A$ the constraints read $x_A=Px_A+r$, where $P$ is the substochastic
matrix of the induced chain and $r$ the vector of one-step probabilities
