of landing in $t_1$. Since $G$ is stopping, that chain reaches a sink
from every vertex of $A$ with probability $1$, so $I-P$ is invertible and
$x_A=(I-P)^{-1}r$ is the unique solution; $w^\ast|_A$ is a solution by
\Cref{thm:transport-sound}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[It decides both published stalls]\label{prop:transport-decides}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item On $G_8$ of \Cref{prop:simorder-stalls} the five average vertices
      are closed under successors, so
      $\operatorname{Sep}(1,4)=w^\ast(4)-w^\ast(1)=-\tfrac1{10}$ and the
      unique controlled vertex is decided --- a comparison that
      \Cref{thm:matching-barrier} shows no sound local matching calculus
      can make.
\item On $H_m$ of \Cref{cor:slack-stalls}, for every $m$,
      $\operatorname{Sep}(c_1,h)=w^\ast(h)-w^\ast(c_1)=-2^{-(m+1)}<0$,
      so the certificate decides at once the pair on which
      \Cref{def:slack} stalls for $2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
In $G_8$ the vertices $0,\dots,4$ are average with successors among
themselves and the sinks; in $H_m$ the set
$\{c_1,\dots,c_{2m},h\}$ is average and closed. Apply
\Cref{lem:transport-exact} and the values recorded in
\Cref{prop:simorder-stalls} and \Cref{thm:vi-lower}. Both separators were
also computed directly from \Cref{def:transport} in exact rational
arithmetic.
\end{proof}

The certificate is nevertheless incomplete, and the obstruction is
exactly the failure of the hypothesis of \Cref{lem:transport-exact}.

The transport polytope is small, and it already has $w^\ast$ as a corner. That
changes what one should expect from it.

\begin{lemma}[Reduction to the controlled coordinates]\label{lem:transport-dim}
Let $G$ be stopping and $C:=\Vmax\cup\Vmin$. Making $C$ and the two sinks
absorbing leaves an average chain that is absorbing, so for every
$u\in\mathbb R^{C}$ there is a unique $\Psi(u)\in\mathbb R^{V}$ agreeing with
$u$ on $C$, vanishing at $t_0$, equal to $1$ at $t_1$, and satisfying the
average equalities; $\Psi$ is affine. Every $x\in Q(G)$ equals
$\Psi(x|_{C})$, so $Q(G)$ is affinely isomorphic to a polytope in
$\mathbb R^{C}$ and $\dim Q(G)\le|C|$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If some non-sink average vertex were not absorbed, the set of vertices from
which neither a sink nor $C$ is reached would be a nonempty set every average
member of which has both successors inside and containing no controlled vertex
--- a trap in the sense of \Cref{lem:trapchar}, contradicting stopping. So the
average system $x_A=Px_A+r$ has $I-P$ invertible and $\Psi$ is the affine map
$u\mapsto(I-P)^{-1}(\,\cdot\,)$ extended by the identity on $C$. The average
rows of $Q(G)$ say exactly that $x$ solves that system.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The objective, not the polytope]\label{thm:transport-objective}
Let $G$ be stopping and let $\sigma:C\to\{0,1\}$ be any profile. Over $Q(G)$
put
\[
  \mathrm{obj}_\sigma(x)\;:=\;
  \sum_{v\in\Vmax}\bigl(x(v)-x(v^{(\sigma(v))})\bigr)
  +\sum_{v\in\Vmin}\bigl(x(v^{(\sigma(v))})-x(v)\bigr).
\]
Then
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item $\mathrm{obj}_\sigma\ge0$ on $Q(G)$, for every $\sigma$;
\item if $\sigma$ is optimal, that is $w^\ast(v)=w^\ast(v^{(\sigma(v))})$ for
      every $v\in C$, then $\min_{Q(G)}\mathrm{obj}_\sigma=0$ and $w^\ast$ is
      its \emph{unique} minimiser;
\item if $\sigma$ is not optimal then $\min_{Q(G)}\mathrm{obj}_\sigma>0$.
\end{enumerate}
In particular $w^\ast$ is a \emph{vertex} of $Q(G)$, and it is cut out by a
linear programme with $N$ variables and $O(N)$ rows --- once the right one of
the $2^{|C|}$ objectives is known.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(a) is the Max and Min rows of $Q(G)$ read termwise. Everything else follows
from one computation. Suppose $x\in Q(G)$ has $\mathrm{obj}_\sigma(x)=0$. Every
term is nonnegative, so $x(v)=x(v^{(\sigma(v))})$ for every $v\in C$. At
$v\in\Vmax$ the rows of $Q(G)$ give $x(v)\ge x(v^{(i)})$ for both $i$, so
$x(v)=\max_ix(v^{(i)})$; at $v\in\Vmin$, dually, $x(v)=\min_ix(v^{(i)})$; and
the average equalities hold by definition of $Q(G)$. Hence $Tx=x$, so
$x=w^\ast$ by \Cref{thm:contraction}, and then
$w^\ast(v)=w^\ast(v^{(\sigma(v))})$ for every $v$, i.e.\ $\sigma$ is optimal.

This gives (c) at once, in contrapositive form. For (b), if $\sigma$ is optimal
then every term vanishes at $w^\ast$, so the minimum is $0$ and is attained
there; and the computation just made shows any other minimiser equals
$w^\ast$. Finally a point that is the unique minimiser of a linear functional
over a polytope is a vertex of it.
\end{proof}

\medskip
The obstruction \Cref{thm:transport-objective} identifies --- the exact set
is $Q(G)$ cut by the $|C|$ \emph{quadratic} complementarity equalities
\[
  q_v(x)\;:=\;\bigl(x(v)-x(v^{(0)})\bigr)\bigl(x(v)-x(v^{(1)})\bigr)\;=\;0,
\]
so the difficulty is a choice among $2^{|C|}$ branches --- has one obvious
remedy: lift the quadratic equalities into a semidefinite programme instead
of branching on them. That is the Lasserre--Shor moment relaxation, and a
positive answer at any fixed degree would put \Cref{prob:main} in
$\mathsf P$. It fails already at the first degree, and it fails in the worst
possible way.

\begin{definition}[The degree-two relaxation]\label{def:lasserre-two}
By \Cref{lem:transport-dim} every $x\in Q(G)$ is determined by its
restriction to $C$, so write $\tilde Q(G)\subseteq\mathbb R^{C}$ for the
image and $g_{v,i}$ for the affine form giving $x(v^{(i)})$ in those
coordinates. The \emph{degree-two relaxation} is the set of pairs
$(u,Y)\in\mathbb R^{C}\times\mathbb R^{C\times C}$ with
\[
  \begin{pmatrix}1&u^{\mathsf T}\\ u&Y\end{pmatrix}\succeq0,\qquad
  u\in\tilde Q(G),\qquad
  L(q_v)=0\ \ (v\in C),
\]
where $L$ is the linear functional sending $1\mapsto1$, $x_i\mapsto u_i$ and
$x_ix_j\mapsto Y_{ij}$. Since $q_v(w^\ast)=0$ and $w^\ast\in\tilde Q(G)$, the
rank-one pair $(w^\ast,w^\ast w^{\ast\mathsf T})$ is always feasible; the
relaxation is \emph{exact} on $G$ if it is the only feasible $u$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Degree two is not merely inexact but vacuous]\label{thm:lasserre-vacuous}
There is a stopping SSG $W_{10}$ on ten vertices with two Max and one Min
vertex, and a one-player stopping SSG $W_{14}$ on fourteen vertices with
three Max vertices, on each of which the degree-two relaxation admits a
feasible point whose $u$ is a maximiser of $x(v_1)$ over $\tilde Q(G)$
itself (the optimal face is two-dimensional in both games). So on those games the lift improves the bound on $x(v_1)$ by
nothing at all, while $w^\ast(v_1)=\tfrac12$ in both.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
$W_{10}$: put $\Vmax=\{v_1,v_2\}$, $\Vmin=\{v_3\}$, $\Vavg=\{e,r,g_1,g_2,g_3\}$ and
\[
 \begin{aligned}
  &v_1\to(v_2,v_3),\quad v_2\to(e,g_1),\quad v_3\to(r,g_1),\quad e\to(t_1,t_0),\\
  &r\to(e,t_0),\quad g_1\to(v_1,g_2),\quad g_2\to(v_1,g_3),\quad g_3\to(v_1,t_0).
 \end{aligned}
\]
Each of $e$, $r$, $g_3$ has a $t_0$ successor, so by \Cref{lem:trapchar} the
greatest trap is empty and the game is stopping; solving,
$w^\ast=(\tfrac12,\tfrac12,\tfrac14)$ on $(v_1,v_2,v_3)$. Its harmonic normal
form --- the first-passage law of each action over $C\cup\{t_1\}$ --- is
\[
  v_1:(e_2;0),\ (e_3;0);\qquad
  v_2:(0;\tfrac12),\ (\tfrac78e_1;0);\qquad
  v_3:(0;\tfrac14),\ (\tfrac78e_1;0),
\]
so $\tilde Q(W_{10})$ is $x_1\ge x_2$, $x_1\ge x_3$, $x_2\ge\tfrac12$,
$x_2\ge\tfrac78x_1$, $x_3\le\tfrac14$, $x_3\le\tfrac78x_1$ and the box, whose
maximum of $x_1$ is $1$, attained on the face
$\{1\}\times[\tfrac78,1]\times[0,\tfrac14]$, at whose vertex
$u:=(1,\tfrac78,\tfrac14)$ we work. Take
\[
  Y:=uu^{\mathsf T}+S,\qquad
  S:=\tfrac1{64}\begin{pmatrix}525&360&360\\360&315&189\\360&189&315\end{pmatrix}.
\]
The moment matrix is $\succeq0$ by the Schur complement, since $S\succ0$: its
leading principal minors are $\tfrac{525}{64}$, $\tfrac{35775}{4096}$ and
$\tfrac{42525}{16384}$. For the equalities, $L(q_v)$ is the value of $q_v$ at
$u$ plus $\alpha_v^{(0)\mathsf T}S\,\alpha_v^{(1)}$, where $\alpha_v^{(i)}$ is
the gradient of $x(v)-g_{v,i}$. At $v_1$ these gradients are $e_1-e_2$ and
$e_1-e_3$, giving $(1-\tfrac78)(1-\tfrac14)=\tfrac3{32}$ and
$\tfrac{525-360-360+189}{64}=-\tfrac3{32}$, which cancel. At $v_2$ the second
factor of $q_{v_2}$ vanishes ($\tfrac78=\tfrac78\cdot1$) and the quadratic term
is $\tfrac{315-\tfrac78\cdot360}{64}=0$; at $v_3$ the first factor vanishes and
the quadratic term is $\tfrac{315-\tfrac78\cdot360}{64}=0$ likewise.

$W_{14}$: put $\Vmax=\{v_1,v_2,v_3\}$, $\Vmin=\emptyset$, and
\[
 \begin{aligned}
  &v_1\to(v_2,v_3),\quad v_2\to(e,g_1),\quad v_3\to(c_1,g_1),\quad e\to(t_1,t_0),\\
  &g_1\to(v_1,g_2),\ g_2\to(v_1,g_3),\ g_3\to(v_1,t_0),\\
  &c_1\to(t_0,c_2),\ c_2\to(t_1,c_3),\ c_3\to(t_1,c_4),\ c_4\to(t_1,c_5),\ c_5\to(t_1,t_0).
 \end{aligned}
\]
It is stopping by the same test, $w^\ast=(\tfrac12,\tfrac12,\tfrac{15}{32})$,
and the normal form is $v_1:(e_2;0),(e_3;0)$; $v_2:(0;\tfrac12),(\tfrac78e_1;0)$;
$v_3:(0;\tfrac{15}{32}),(\tfrac78e_1;0)$. The maximum of $x_1$ over
$\tilde Q(W_{14})$ is again $1$, attained on the face
$\{1\}\times[\tfrac78,1]^{2}$, at whose vertex $u:=(1,\tfrac78,\tfrac78)$ we
work,
and with
$S:=\tfrac1{128}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}175&120&120\\120&105&63\\120&63&105\end{smallmatrix}\right)$,
whose leading minors are $\tfrac{175}{128}$, $\tfrac{3975}{16384}$,
$\tfrac{1575}{131072}$, the three equalities read
$(1-\tfrac78)^2+\tfrac{175-120-120+63}{128}=\tfrac1{64}-\tfrac1{64}=0$ and
$\tfrac{105-\tfrac78\cdot120}{128}=0$ twice.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:lasserre}
Three readings (throughout, ``hard family'' means the families of
\Cref{sec:gap}; the single stall instances $R$ of \Cref{prop:own-stall} and
$G^{\ast}$ of \Cref{prop:simorder-stalls} have $|C|=5$ and $7$). First, the failure is not a matter of two players: $W_{14}$
is one-player, so the degree-two relaxation is already inexact on transient
two-action Markov decision processes, where the value is computed by a
linear programme (\Cref{thm:one-player}). The lift is therefore not weak
because the problem is hard; it is weak because complementarity at three
vertices already outruns one moment matrix.

Second, the theorem is stated in the vacuous form on purpose. Non-exactness
alone would leave open that the lift improves the bound somewhat, and a
bound improving by a fixed factor per degree would still be worth having.
What the two games show is that in the direction $x(v_1)$ --- and $v_1$ is
value-distinguishing in both --- the degree-two lift returns the linear
programme's own answer.

Third, what this does and does not close. Exactness at any \emph{fixed}
degree $2t$ would give a semidefinite programme of size $N^{O(t)}$ whose
optimum is $w^\ast(v_0)$, and with the denominator bound of
\Cref{lem:denominator-sharp} that would decide \Cref{prob:main} in
polynomial time; so a negative answer at degree two was the expected one and
is not surprising. Degree four is not refuted here, and by the same
reduction any conjecture that some fixed degree \emph{is} exact is
target-equivalent and must not be made. Nor is the failure an argument
against convex lifts in general: \Cref{thm:hybrid-convex-barrier} already
describes what a shrinking chain of convex certificates would have to do,
and \Cref{thm:lasserre-vacuous} says only that the first member of the
Lasserre chain is not such a certificate.

Finally, and this bounds the result's significance sharply: the two
witnesses have $|C|=3$, and \emph{every} hard family in this document has
$|C|\le2$ --- the wedge $WD(e,j,m)$ of \Cref{def:wedge} and
$\mathrm{CC}(L,m)$ have two controlled vertices, and $G8$, $S$, $S_r$,
$H_m$ and $G_m$ have at most one. So \Cref{thm:lasserre-vacuous} touches
none of the instances on which the mechanisms of \Cref{sec:gap} are known
to stall --- indeed the round-$15$ verification route proved, and its
auditor confirmed, that the degree-two lift is \emph{exact} on every
stopping SSG with $|C|\le2$, with no nondegeneracy hypothesis, so on those
instances it returns the value --- and it is not evidence that the lift is
weak where the problem is hard. It is evidence that it is weak already
where the problem is easy.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The linear lift in the choice variables fails one level later]\label{rem:choice-lift}
The round-$17$ convex-lift route applied the other standard hierarchy to
the same system: write each controlled row as
$x(v)=y_vx(v^{(0)})+(1-y_v)x(v^{(1)})$ with a choice variable
$y_v\in[0,1]$ and take the level-$j$ Sherali--Adams
(reformulation--linearisation) lift $R_j(G)$ over the choice variables, a
linear programme of size $N^{O(j)}$ whose level zero is $Q(G)$ itself
(Sherali and Adams, from memory, unchecked; the level-one set is Balas'
disjunctive closure $\bigcap_{v\in C}\operatorname{conv}(F_v^{0}\cup F_v^{1})$
of the faces $F_v^{i}:=\{x\in Q(G):x(v)=x(v^{(i)})\}$). Level one is not
exact, and $W_{14}$ is again the witness: in the coordinates
$u=(x(v_1),x(v_2),x(v_3))$ the point
$u=(\tfrac35,\tfrac{23}{40},\tfrac{11}{20})$ lies in $R_1(W_{14})$, being
$\tfrac{13}{20}(\tfrac8{13},\tfrac8{13},\tfrac7{13})+\tfrac7{20}(\tfrac47,\tfrac12,\tfrac47)$
with the two points tight at the two actions of $v_1$, and
$\tfrac45(\tfrac12,\tfrac12,\tfrac{15}{32})+\tfrac15(1,\tfrac78,\tfrac78)$
with the two points tight at both actions of $v_2$ and of $v_3$, all five
in $\tilde Q(W_{14})$; so $\max_{R_1}x(v_1)\ge\tfrac35>\tfrac12=w^\ast(v_1)$,
strictly between the transport bound $1$ and the value (the route reports
the maximum as exactly $\tfrac35$ and level two as exact; the certificate
was re-verified here, \texttt{scripts/round17-verify/cl\_verify.py}).
Joining $W_{14}$ to its dual under the gadget of
\Cref{thm:compare-equivalence} makes the level-one interval at the root
$[\tfrac9{20},\tfrac{11}{20}]$, straddling the threshold: the two-sided
failure the second paragraph above asks for, on a $27$-vertex game that is
two one-player games. What the route reported the hierarchy to give ---
$\max_{R_j}x=w^\ast$ once $j\ge\min(|\Vmax|,\rho_{\max}+1)$ and
$\min_{R_j}x=w^\ast$ once $j\ge\min(|\Vmin|,\rho_{\min}+1)$, with $\rho$ the
number of controlled vertices of that player whose successor difference
changes sign on $Q(G)$ --- it did not prove, and it is recorded here as a
claim; it would in any case be dominated by enumerating the $2^{\rho}$ choices of the smaller side
and solving one-player games, so no class comes of it, and exactness at
any fixed level is target-equivalent and is not conjectured.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The complementarity route, and why we do not pursue it]\label{rem:lcp}
The same fixed-point system is a linear complementarity problem, and its
matrix is a $P$-matrix; interior-point methods solve $\mathrm{LCP}(M,q)$ in
$O\bigl((1+\kappa)\sqrt n\,L\bigr)$ iterations when $M$ is sufficient with
handicap $\kappa$, so a polynomially bounded handicap would decide
\Cref{prob:main}. We do not develop this, because it is not ours and it is
closed. The reduction of turn-based stochastic games to $P$-matrix LCPs and
the computation of the interior-point bounds for them is Hansen and
Ibsen-Jensen's, after Jurdzi\'nski and Savani for the deterministic case and
Stickney and Watson for the LCP--orientation correspondence this document
meets again in \Cref{prop:allsw-auso}; that the handicap of a sufficient
matrix can be exponential in its size, so that those bounds are not
polynomial, is de~Klerk and E.-Nagy's; and the invariance of the handicap
under principal pivot transforms, and the characterisation of the LCPs
solvable by a single linear programme, are older still. A round of this
project reconstructed all of that independently before the attributions were
identified, which is recorded here so that it is not reconstructed again.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:transport-objective}
This is a reformulation and not progress, and it is worth being exact about
which. Choosing the objective in \Cref{thm:transport-objective} means naming an
optimal profile, which is \textsc{SSG-Value} itself by
\Cref{thm:compare-equivalence}; so (b) and (c) together say ``the problem is in
$\mathsf P$ if and only if it is''. What they do add is structural, and it
redirects the search. The relaxation $Q(G)$ is not a coarse outer
approximation that additional valid inequalities would whittle down to a point:
it is at most $|C|$-dimensional (\Cref{lem:transport-dim}) and already has
$w^\ast$ among its vertices, so the vertex one wants is present and the whole
question is which linear functional selects it.

We do \emph{not} conclude from this that cutting planes cannot help, and the
temptation to do so should be resisted: a cut that removes other vertices of
$Q(G)$ while keeping $w^\ast$ is perfectly consistent with everything above,
and \Cref{sec:hybrid} is exactly such a procedure. What the theorem rules out
is only the diagnosis that $Q(G)$ is too coarse \emph{at $w^\ast$}: it is not,
$w^\ast$ being a vertex. The difficulty has moved entirely into choosing among
the $2^{|C|}$ objectives.
Verified on $100$ random stopping games with $|C|\le5$ (the generator's
size range was not recorded), all $2^{|C|}$ profiles of each:
no violation of (a), (b) or (c), with uniqueness in (b) tested in the strongest
form, by maximising and minimising every coordinate subject to
$\mathrm{obj}_\sigma=0$.
\end{remark}


\begin{proposition}[A seven-vertex stall]\label{prop:transport-stalls}
Let $S$ be the SSG on $\{v,a,b,b_1,b_2\}$ with $t_0,t_1$, where
$v\in\Vmax$ and $a,b,b_1,b_2\in\Vavg$, and
\[
  v\to(a,b),\quad a\to(t_1,t_0),\quad b\to(v,b_1),\quad
  b_1\to(b_2,t_0),\quad b_2\to(t_1,t_0).
\]
Then $S$ is stopping, $w^\ast=(\tfrac12,\tfrac12,\tfrac38,\tfrac14,\tfrac12)$,
so the two successors of the only controlled vertex have different
values, and yet
$\operatorname{Sep}(a,b)=\operatorname{Sep}(b,a)=+\tfrac18$: the certificate
does not order the two successors against each other. It does nevertheless
decide $v$, by $\operatorname{Sep}(v,b)=-\tfrac18<0$; see
\Cref{rem:own-successor}, which corrects the reading of this proposition and of
\Cref{thm:transport-barrier}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A trap avoids the sinks, hence omits $a$ and $b_2$, hence $b_1$, hence
$b$, hence $v$; so $S$ is stopping by \Cref{lem:trapchar}. The values are
immediate. For the separator, every $x\in Q(S)$ is determined by
$t:=x(v)$: the average equalities give $x(a)=\tfrac12$,
$x(b_2)=\tfrac12$, $x(b_1)=\tfrac14$ and
$x(b)=\tfrac12(t+\tfrac14)=\tfrac t2+\tfrac18$, while the two Max rows
give $t\ge\tfrac12$ and $t\ge\tfrac14$; with $x\le1$ the feasible set is
$t\in[\tfrac12,1]$. Then $x(b)-x(a)=\tfrac t2-\tfrac38$ runs from
$-\tfrac18$ at $t=\tfrac12$ to $+\tfrac18$ at $t=1$, so both maxima are
$+\tfrac18$. For the last claim, $x(b)-x(v)=\tfrac18-\tfrac t2$ is maximised at
$t=\tfrac12$, giving $\operatorname{Sep}(v,b)=-\tfrac18$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Compare a controlled vertex with its own successor]\label{rem:own-successor}
A decision rule in the sense of \Cref{def:decision-rule} must name a controlled
vertex and say which successor is larger. It need not do so by ordering the two
successors against each other, and for the mechanisms of this section it should
not: at $v\in\Vmax$ the row $x(v)\ge x(v^{(i)})$ belongs to $Q(G)$, so
\[
  \operatorname{Sep}\bigl(v,v^{(i)}\bigr)\;\le\;0\quad\text{always},\qquad
  \operatorname{Sep}\bigl(v,v^{(i)}\bigr)\;\ge\;\val(v^{(i)})-\val(v)
\]
by \Cref{thm:transport-sound}, and the right-hand side is $0$ at an optimal
successor. Hence $\operatorname{Sep}(v,v^{(i)})<0$ \emph{proves} $v^{(i)}$ is
not optimal and therefore decides $v$; at $v\in\Vmin$ the same holds with the
inequalities reversed. The pair test $\operatorname{Sep}(v^{(0)},v^{(1)})$ is
a different test, and the two are incomparable. In one direction the
difference is not marginal: on $S$ above the pair test
returns $+\tfrac18$ in both directions while $\operatorname{Sep}(v,b)=-\tfrac18$
decides $v$ outright, and on the family $S_r$ of \Cref{thm:transport-barrier}
the pair test returns $2^{-r-2}>0$ while $\operatorname{Sep}(v,b_0)=-2^{-r-2}<0$
for every $r$, verified exactly for $r=2,\dots,5$. In the other direction, on
the ten-vertex game $R$ of \Cref{prop:own-stall} every clause-(ii)
separator is $0$, clause (i) firing only at the vertex the seed pins, while the non-strict pair test ---
$\operatorname{Sep}(v^{(0)},v^{(1)})\le0$ or
$\operatorname{Sep}(v^{(1)},v^{(0)})\le0$, sound by \Cref{thm:transport-sound}
and naming an optimal successor --- decides all three value-distinguishing
controlled vertices, through the rows $x\ge0$ and $x(6)\le0$ that pin one
successor at a sink or in $Z_0$ (the round-$17$ convex-lift audit,
correcting an earlier ``strictly weaker'' here; recomputed,
\texttt{scripts/round17-verify/pairtest.py}).

The principle is not confined to linear programmes, and for a mechanism that
propagates two-sided facts there are two ways to fire. At $v\in\Vmax$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=2.4em]
\item a derivation of $w^\ast(v)\le w^\ast(v^{(i)})$ forces \emph{equality},
      since the reverse inequality is automatic, so it proves $v^{(i)}$
      \emph{optimal};
\item a derivation of $w^\ast(v^{(i)})<w^\ast(v)$ proves $v^{(i)}$ \emph{not}
      optimal, hence the other successor is.
\end{enumerate}
So for an order-only mechanism the test is whether $v\preceq v^{(i)}$, and for
the slack calculus whether $\Delta_k(v,v^{(i)})\le0$ or
$\Delta_k(v^{(i)},v)<0$; note that (i) is non-strict, and so is the pair test's
$\operatorname{Sep}(v^{(0)},v^{(1)})\le0$; both decide. For \Cref{def:transport} only (ii) is
available, in the form $\operatorname{Sep}(v,v^{(i)})<0$ displayed above: the
Max row makes $\operatorname{Sep}(v^{(i)},v)\ge0$ always, so (i) fires there
exactly when the seeded polytope forces $x(v)=x(v^{(i)})$, as the free seed
does at every controlled vertex of $Z_0\cup Z_1$. Both directions dualise at $\Vmin$.

We accordingly re-examined the other negative results of this document against
this test, in both directions, and they survive. On $G_8$ the simulation
preorder relates neither $(5,4)$ nor $(5,1)$, so \Cref{prop:simorder-stalls}
holds for the own-successor test as well; a preorder carries no strictness, so
(ii) is unavailable to it in any case. On $H_m$ the slack calculus first fires
--- by (i) and by (ii) alike --- at rounds $15,39,101,250$ for $m=3,\dots,6$,
exactly one round after the pair test's $14,38,100,249$, so
\Cref{cor:slack-stalls} is unaffected. Only the claims resting on
\Cref{def:transport} needed correcting.

So \Cref{prop:transport-stalls} and \Cref{thm:transport-barrier} are statements
about \emph{ordering the two successors}, which is what they prove, and not
about deciding the vertex, which the certificate does at once on both families.
The same correction applies to \Cref{rem:transport} and to
\Cref{thm:hybrid-lower}, and it is the reason
\Cref{rem:hybrid-lower-not-a-refutation} has to be stated: a lower bound on the
entry $\Delta_k(v^{(0)},v^{(1)})$ says nothing about the entry
$\Delta_k(v^{(i)},v)$, and it is the latter that drives the rule.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[The own-successor test does stall]\label{prop:own-stall}
Let $R$ be the SSG on $\{0,\dots,7\}$ with $t_0=8$, $t_1=9$,
$\Vmin=\{0,1,6,7\}$, $\Vmax=\{4\}$, $\Vavg=\{2,3,5\}$ and
\[
  \begin{aligned}
   &0\to(2,5), &&1\to(5,3), &&2\to(5,2), &&3\to(0,t_1),\\
   &4\to(0,t_0), &&5\to(t_1,t_0), &&6\to(0,t_0), &&7\to(5,2).
  \end{aligned}
\]
Then $R$ is stopping,
$w^\ast=(\tfrac12,\tfrac12,\tfrac12,\tfrac34,\tfrac12,\tfrac12,0,\tfrac12)$,
$Z_0=\{6,t_0\}$ and $Z_1=\{t_1\}$, the value-distinguishing controlled vertices
are $1$, $4$ and $6$, and the own-successor test of \Cref{rem:own-successor},
run over $Q(R;L,U)$ with the free seed $U:=0$ on $Z_0$ and $L:=1$ on $Z_1$,
returns $0$ at all six clause-(ii) separators, and its clause (i) fires only
at $6$, which the free seed $Z_0$ already pins to $0$. So that reading decides
no controlled vertex outside the free seed; the non-strict pair test does
decide all three (\Cref{rem:own-successor}), so $R$ stalls the own-successor
reading only, at its two value-distinguishing vertices outside $Z_0$, and the
seven-vertex game of \Cref{rem:own-stall} keeps one vertex silent under both
readings.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Stopping: a trap avoids $t_1$, hence omits $5$ (average, one successor $t_1$)
and $3$; then $2$ (average, both successors would have to lie inside) and $7$;
then $0$, $1$, $4$ and $6$, each of which needs a successor inside. The values
are immediate: $w^\ast(5)=\tfrac12$; $w^\ast(2)=\tfrac12(w^\ast(5)+w^\ast(2))$
forces $w^\ast(2)=w^\ast(5)=\tfrac12$; $w^\ast(0)=\min(\tfrac12,\tfrac12)$;
$w^\ast(3)=\tfrac12(w^\ast(0)+1)=\tfrac34$;
$w^\ast(1)=\min(\tfrac12,\tfrac34)=\tfrac12$;
$w^\ast(4)=\max(\tfrac12,0)=\tfrac12$; $w^\ast(6)=\min(\tfrac12,0)=0$;
$w^\ast(7)=\tfrac12$.

For the separators, every $x\in Q(R;L,U)$ has $x(5)=\tfrac12$ from the average
row at $5$, then $x(2)=x(5)=\tfrac12$ from the average row at $2$, and
$x(3)=\tfrac12(x(0)+1)$. The Min rows give $x(0)\le\tfrac12$, $x(1)\le x(5)$,
$x(1)\le x(3)$, $x(6)\le x(0)$, $x(6)\le0$ and $x(7)\le\tfrac12$; the Max rows
give $x(4)\ge x(0)$ and $x(4)\ge0$; the seed adds $x(6)\le0$, which the Min row
already implies. Nothing bounds $x(0)$ or $x(4)$ from below except $x\ge0$. So
$x(0)=x(1)=x(4)=x(6)=0$, $x(2)=x(5)=\tfrac12$, $x(3)=\tfrac12$, $x(7)=0$ is
feasible; so is the point with $x(0)=0$ and $x(1)=\tfrac12$, at which
$x(3)=\tfrac12=x(1)$. The six separators are therefore all attained at $0$: at
the Min vertex $1$ they are $\max\{x(1)-x(5)\}$ and $\max\{x(1)-x(3)\}$, both
$\le0$ by the Min rows and both met at the second point; at the Max vertex $4$
they are $\max\{x(0)-x(4)\}$ and $\max\{-x(4)\}$, both $\le0$ by the Max rows
and both met at the first; and at the Min vertex $6$ they are
$\max\{x(6)-x(0)\}$ and $\max\{x(6)\}$, and $x(6)=0$ throughout. None is
negative, so clause (ii) decides no vertex; clause (i) ---
$\max\{x(v)-x(v^{(i)})\}\le0$ at a Min vertex --- fires at $6$ alone, where
$x(6)=0=x(t_0)$ throughout: its separators are $\tfrac12,\tfrac34$ at $1$,
$1,1$ at $4$ and $\tfrac12,0$ at $6$ (the round-$18$ paper audit; recomputed,
\texttt{scripts/round18-verify/own\_r.py}).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:own-stall}
\Cref{prop:own-stall} is a stall of the own-successor reading, not of the
certificate: read as the non-strict pair test, the same programme decides
every value-distinguishing vertex of $R$ (\Cref{rem:own-successor}). What
\Cref{prop:transport-stalls} was originally asserting --- that the transport
certificate alone, read in all three ways and seeded with everything that is
free, is not a decision rule --- is \emph{not} restored by the smaller game
below, which is a silence at one vertex and not a stall of the rule: on the
stopping SSG with $\Vavg=\{0,2\}$, $\Vmin=\{1\}$, $\Vmax=\{3,4\}$,
$0\to(2,t_1)$, $1\to(3,t_1)$, $2\to(t_0,0)$, $3\to(0,t_0)$, $4\to(1,2)$,
$w^\ast=(\tfrac23,\tfrac23,\tfrac13,\tfrac23,\tfrac23)$, $Z_0=\{t_0\}$,
$Z_1=\{t_1\}$, the average rows pin $x(0)=\tfrac23$ and $x(2)=\tfrac13$, and
at the value-distinguishing Max vertex $4$ both clause-(ii) separators
are $0$ (attained at $x(4)=x(1)=\tfrac23$ and at $x(4)=x(2)=\tfrac13$,
$x(1)=0$), the clause-(i) separators are $1$ and $\tfrac23$, and $x(1)-x(2)$
ranges over $[-\tfrac13,\tfrac23]$, so all three readings are silent there;
but in the same round vertex $3$ is decided by clause (ii)
($\operatorname{Sep}(3,t_0)=-\tfrac23$) and vertex $1$ by the pair test
($\operatorname{Sep}(3,t_1)=0$), and once those two are retyped as in
\Cref{thm:decide-one-bit} the certificate decides $4$ as well
($\operatorname{Sep}(4,2)=-\tfrac13$): the rule, iterated, solves the game
in two rounds, and a stall proper --- every reading silent at every
value-distinguishing vertex of a stopping game --- is exhibited nowhere in
this document (the round-$17$ convex-lift audit found the game, the
round-$18$ paper audit its two decided vertices; recomputed,
\texttt{scripts/round18-verify/own\_r.py}). The mechanism of the silence is visible
in the proof of \Cref{prop:own-stall} and is the same
non-convexity the whole section turns on. At a Min vertex $Q(G)$ imposes only
$x(v)\le x(v^{(i)})$, so $x(v)$ can be pushed \emph{down} to touch either
successor, and the programme cannot see which one attains the minimum; at
vertex $1$ both separators are therefore $0$ although $w^\ast(5)<w^\ast(3)$.
Dually at a Max vertex nothing bounds $x(v)$ from below, which is why vertex
$4$ survives. What the seed removes is only the part of that freedom that
$Z_0$ and $Z_1$ already pin, and here it removes none of it.

The instance is small but not accidental: hill climbing on the fraction of
value-distinguishing controlled vertices left undecided reached $1$
repeatedly, on non-degenerate witnesses that we do not print (the
generator was not kept, so that claim is a report). Every stall we found
before adding the seed was an artefact of discarding it, which is why the
seed is stated as part of the test.
\end{remark}


\begin{theorem}[The stall survives every polynomial seed]\label{thm:transport-barrier}
For $r\ge2$ let $S_r$ be the stopping SSG on
$\{v,a,b_0,\dots,b_{r+1}\}$, $N=r+6$, with $v\in\Vmax$ and every other
non-sink vertex average, and
\[
  v\to(a,b_0),\quad a\to(t_1,t_0),\quad b_i\to(v,b_{i+1})\ (0\le i\le r-1),
  \quad b_r\to(b_{r+1},t_0),\quad b_{r+1}\to(t_1,t_0).
\]
Then $w^\ast(v)=w^\ast(a)=\tfrac12$ and
$w^\ast(b_i)=\tfrac12-2^{-(r-i)-2}$ for $0\le i\le r$ (and
$w^\ast(b_{r+1})=\tfrac12$), so $v$ distinguishes values by
$2^{-r-2}=2^{-(N-4)}$. Writing $x^{(t)}$ for the unique point of $Q(S_r)$
with $x(v)=t$, and $\bar t:=\max\{t:x^{(t)}\in Q(S_r;L,U)\}$, the
certificate decides the pair $(a,b_0)$ if and only if
$\bar t\le t_{\mathrm{rev}}:=\tfrac12+\tfrac1{4(2^{r}-1)}$, the reverse
direction $\operatorname{Sep}(b_0,a)=2^{-r-2}>0$ never deciding. In particular the
unseeded certificate does not decide it, and neither does the certificate
seeded by $k$ rounds of value iteration for any $k<2^{\,r-2}=2^{\,N-8}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The average equalities determine every coordinate from $t=x(v)$: one
computes $x^{(t)}(a)=\tfrac12$, $x^{(t)}(b_{r+1})=\tfrac12$,
$x^{(t)}(b_r)=\tfrac14$ and, downwards,
$x^{(t)}(b_i)=t\bigl(1-2^{-(r-i)}\bigr)+2^{-(r-i)-2}$; taking $i=0$,
$x^{(t)}(b_0)=t(1-2^{-r})+2^{-r-2}$, which equals $x^{(t)}(a)=\tfrac12$
exactly at $t=t_{\mathrm{rev}}$ and exceeds it above. Since $Q$ is a
segment in $t$, the maximum of $x(b_0)-x(a)$ is attained at $\bar t$ and
is at most $0$ exactly when $\bar t\le t_{\mathrm{rev}}$; in the other
direction $\max\{x(a)-x(b_0)\}$ is attained at $t=\tfrac12$ and equals
$2^{-r-2}>0$. Feasibility forces
$t\ge x^{(t)}(a)=\tfrac12$ and $t\ge x^{(t)}(b_0)$, i.e.\ $t\ge\tfrac12$;
the upper end is cut only by $U$. For the value-iteration seed one has
$u_k(v)\ge(1-2^{-r})^{\lceil k/2\rceil}$, which exceeds
$t_{\mathrm{rev}}$ for every $k<2^{\,r-2}$ when $r\ge3$. The values, the
separators for $r=2,3,4,5$ --- namely $\operatorname{Sep}(a,b_0)=\tfrac5{16},\tfrac{13}{32},\tfrac{29}{64},\tfrac{61}{128}$
and $\operatorname{Sep}(b_0,a)=\tfrac1{16},\tfrac1{32},\tfrac1{64},\tfrac1{128}$ ---
and the thresholds $t_{\mathrm{rev}}=\tfrac7{12},\tfrac{15}{28},\tfrac{31}{60},\tfrac{63}{124}$
were computed exactly.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:transport}
The two barriers are complementary, and together they say something
sharper than either alone. \Cref{thm:slack-barrier} defeats the slack
calculus on parts that are \emph{separated}: two disjoint successor-closed
regions between which nothing can be transmitted except by value
iteration. \Cref{lem:transport-exact} shows that on exactly such parts the
transport certificate is not merely fast but \emph{exact}, which is why
it disposes of $H_m$ and of $G_8$ in one linear program. What defeats it
instead is the opposite configuration: in $S$ and in $S_r$ the controlled
vertex $v$ lies \emph{on a cycle through the branch it must be compared
against}, so no average part containing $b_0$ is successor-closed, the
polytope is a genuine interval rather than a point, and the comparison
turns on where in that interval $w^\ast(v)$ sits --- which is the question
one started from. \Cref{thm:transport-barrier} makes the quantitative
form of this precise: to order the pair $(a,b_0)$ the certificate needs its
seed to localise $w^\ast(v)$ to within $2^{-\Theta(N)}$, and value iteration,
the only seed available in polynomial time, takes $2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds to get
there. As with $H_m$, the instance itself is easy --- $S_r$ is one-player
and \Cref{thm:one-player} solves it by an explicit linear program --- so
what would be refuted is the mechanism, not the tractability of the family.

We stress the qualification just inserted, because without it this remark is
wrong. What is exponentially hard here is \emph{ordering the two successors
against each other}. Deciding the vertex is not: by \Cref{rem:own-successor},
$\operatorname{Sep}(v,b_0)=-2^{-r-2}<0$ on every $S_r$, so the unseeded
certificate decides $v$ in one linear program. The complementarity recorded
above is real and is about the pair test. A stall of the own-successor test
does exist, but it takes a different instance: \Cref{prop:own-stall} exhibits
one, on ten vertices, at which that test seeded with $Z_0$ and $Z_1$ decides
nothing outside the seed --- while the pair test decides everything there,
the two readings being incomparable --- and \Cref{rem:own-stall} exhibits a
seven-vertex game with one vertex silent under both.
\end{remark}


\subsection{Freezing one vertex: the response map folds}\label{sec:fold}

Every mechanism above compares two vertices of one fixed game. A different and
equally natural idea is to change the game instead: freeze one vertex at a
prescribed payoff and watch the value move. For a non-sink $u$ and
$\theta\in[0,1]$ write $G[u{:=}\theta]$ for the game in which $u$'s two
out-edges are deleted and $u$ is declared a sink of payoff $\theta$. This is
legitimate inside \Cref{def:ssg}: the results of
\Cref{sec:operators,sec:stopping} used below hold for terminals with
arbitrary constant payoffs (\Cref{rem:terminals}); and for dyadic
$\theta=k/2^{m}$ the construction can be made literal by replacing $u$
with a chain of $m$ average vertices realising $\theta$ from $t_0,t_1$
through its binary expansion, a gadget we describe but have not needed to
build.
Under a fixed positional pair the value at any vertex is an affine function of
$\theta$ whose slope is a hitting probability, so
\[
  R_{u,v}(\theta)\;:=\;\val_{G[u:=\theta]}(v)
\]
is piecewise affine, monotone and $1$-Lipschitz, and its pieces are exactly the
regions on which the optimal pair is constant. Any parametric or continuation
method that sweeps $\theta$ and recomputes at each breakpoint costs at least the
number of pieces, and its cost is polynomial only if that number is. It is not.

\begin{theorem}[The response map has exponentially many pieces]\label{thm:fold}
For $D\ge1$ let $P_D$ be the SSG with non-sink vertices $u$,
$k_1,\dots,k_{2D-1}$ and $X_d,Y_d,F_d,G_d$ for $1\le d\le D$, where
$u,k_j,X_d,Y_d\in\Vavg$, $F_d\in\Vmax$, $G_d\in\Vmin$, and
\[
  \begin{aligned}
   &u\to(t_0,t_1),\qquad k_j\to(t_0,k_{j+1})\ (j<2D-1),\qquad k_{2D-1}\to(t_0,t_1),\\
   &X_d\to(t_0,F_{d-1}),\qquad Y_d\to(k_{2D-2d+1},G_{d-1}),\qquad
    F_d\to(X_d,Y_d),\qquad G_d\to(X_d,Y_d),
  \end{aligned}
\]
under the conventions $F_0:=u$ and $G_0:=t_0$. Then $P_D$ is stopping, has
$|V|=6D+2$, and with $T(x):=|2x-1|$ the tent map,
\[
  \val_{P_D[u:=\theta]}(F_D)\;=\;2^{-(D+1)}\Bigl(\theta+1-2^{-D}+2^{-D}T^{D}(\theta)\Bigr)
  \qquad(\theta\in[0,1]).
\]
Consequently $R_{u,F_D}$ has exactly $2^{D}=2^{(|V|-2)/6}$ affine pieces, with
breakpoints exactly at $j2^{-D}$ for $1\le j\le2^{D}-1$ and slopes alternating
between $0$ and $2^{-D}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Deleting $u$'s out-edges leaves an acyclic digraph --- every out-edge of
$X_d,Y_d,F_d,G_d$ goes to a sink, to a chain vertex, or to level $d-1$, and
every $k_j$ goes to $t_0$ or $k_{j+1}$ --- so $P_D[u{:=}\theta]$ is a stopping
game with terminal payoffs and its values are computed by backward induction.
Along the chain $\val(k_{2D-1})=\tfrac12$ and $\val(k_j)=\tfrac12\val(k_{j+1})$,
so $\val(k_j)=2^{-(2D-j)}$ and $\val(k_{2D-2d+1})=2^{-(2d-1)}$.

Write $f_d:=\val(F_d)$, $g_d:=\val(G_d)$, so $f_0=\theta$ and $g_0=0$. Backward
induction gives $\val(X_d)=\tfrac12f_{d-1}$ and
$\val(Y_d)=\tfrac12\bigl(2^{-(2d-1)}+g_{d-1}\bigr)=4^{-d}+\tfrac12g_{d-1}$, and
then $f_d=\max(\val(X_d),\val(Y_d))$, $g_d=\min(\val(X_d),\val(Y_d))$ --- the
two controlled vertices share the same pair of successors, which is the whole
device. Since $\max+\min$ is the sum and $\max-\min$ the absolute difference,
$s_d:=f_d+g_d$ and $\varphi_d:=f_d-g_d$ obey
\[
  s_d=\tfrac12 s_{d-1}+4^{-d},\qquad
  \varphi_d=\bigl|\tfrac12\varphi_{d-1}-4^{-d}\bigr| .
\]
From $\varphi_0=\theta=T^{0}(\theta)$ and $\varphi_{d-1}=4^{-(d-1)}T^{d-1}(\theta)$
one gets
$\varphi_d=\bigl|2\cdot4^{-d}T^{d-1}(\theta)-4^{-d}\bigr|=4^{-d}T^{d}(\theta)$;
and $s_0=\theta$ gives $s_d=2^{-d}(\theta+1-2^{-d})$ by induction. Then
$f_D=\tfrac12(s_D+\varphi_D)$ is the displayed formula.

For the piece count, $T$ is affine of slope $-2$ on $[0,\tfrac12]$ and $+2$ on
$[\tfrac12,1]$, each restriction a bijection onto $[0,1]$. Inductively $T^{D}$
is affine exactly on the intervals $[j2^{-D},(j+1)2^{-D}]$, with slopes
$\pm2^{D}$ alternating, the alternation persisting across $\theta=\tfrac12$
because the two halves are mirror images. So $f_D$ has slope
$2^{-(D+1)}(1\pm1)$, alternately $0$ and $2^{-D}$; consecutive pieces differ, so
the count is exactly $2^{D}$. Finally $0\le f_D\le2^{-(D+1)}(2+2^{-D})<1$, so
all values lie in $[0,1]$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:fold}
The mechanism is a fold, and it is cheap: one Max and one Min vertex sharing the
same two successors compute $\max$ and $\min$ of the same pair, whose difference
is $|o_0-o_1|$, and one such pair doubles the number of monotone stretches.
Every constant the construction needs sits on a single shared chain, so $D$
folds cost $O(D)$ vertices. What the theorem rules out is the parametric route
in the form that motivates it: sweeping $\theta$ and tracking the optimal pair
meets $2^{\Omega(N)}$ distinct pairs, so no continuation method of that shape is
polynomial. It does \emph{not} rule out parametric methods that avoid tracking
the pair, and we claim nothing about those. The identity was verified against
backward induction in exact rational arithmetic at every dyadic grid point,
every midpoint and five off-grid rationals for $D=1,\dots,12$, with no mismatch
and the slope set exactly $\{0,2^{-D}\}$; $P_{12}$ has $74$ vertices and
$4096$ pieces.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[The stopping-probability path is exponentially long]\label{prop:discount-fold}
For $D\ge1$ let $\mathrm{BP}(D)$ be the SSG with non-sink vertices
$F_0,f_0$, $c_1$, $w^{d}_1,w^{d}_2,w^{d}_3$ for $2\le d\le D$ and
$P_d,Q_d,B_d,X_d,Y_d,F_d,G_d$ for $1\le d\le D$, all average except
$F_d\in\Vmax$ and $G_d\in\Vmin$, with $G_0:=t_0$, $c_d:=w^{d}_1$ and
\[
 \begin{aligned}
  &F_0\to(f_0,f_0),\quad f_0\to(t_1,t_1),\quad c_1\to(t_0,t_1),\quad
   w^{d}_1\to(t_0,w^{d}_2),\ \ w^{d}_2\to(t_0,w^{d}_3),\ \ w^{d}_3\to(c_{d-1},c_{d-1}),\\
  &P_d\to(F_{d-1},F_{d-1}),\quad Q_d\to(G_{d-1},G_{d-1}),\quad B_d\to(c_d,c_d),\\
  &X_d\to(P_d,t_0),\quad Y_d\to(Q_d,B_d),\quad F_d\to(X_d,Y_d),\quad G_d\to(X_d,Y_d).
 \end{aligned}
\]
It is acyclic, hence stopping, on $N=10D+2$ vertices with $D$ Max and $D$
Min vertices ($G_D$ is not reachable from $F_D$, so the game rooted at
$F_D$ has $D-1$ Min vertices), and in $\mathrm{BP}(D)_{\varrho}$
(\Cref{def:discount-path}),
with $\kappa_d:=\val(c_d)$, $\varphi_d:=\val(F_d)-\val(G_d)$ and
$e_d:=\varrho(\varrho^{3}/4)^{d}$,
\[
  \kappa_d=\tfrac{\varrho}2\bigl(\tfrac{\varrho^{3}}4\bigr)^{d-1}=\tfrac12e_{d-1},\qquad
  \varphi_d=\tfrac{\varrho^{3}}2\,\bigl|\varphi_{d-1}-\kappa_d\bigr|,\qquad
  \frac{\varphi_d}{e_d}=T^{d}(\varrho)\qquad(0<\varrho<1),
\]
$T(z)=|2z-1|$ the tent map of \Cref{thm:fold}. Consequently $F_d$ and $G_d$
reverse exactly at the $\varrho$ with $T^{d-1}(\varrho)=\tfrac12$, the odd
multiples of $2^{-d}$; these sets are disjoint across $d$; the breakpoint
set of the path is exactly $\{k2^{-D}:1\le k\le2^{D}-1\}$, so that
$B(\mathrm{BP}(D))=2^{D}-1=2^{(N-2)/10}-1$; the path meets $2^{D}$ pairwise
distinct optimal pairs, its itinerary being the symbolic dynamics of $T$;
the single vertex $F_D$ reverses $2^{D-1}$ times; and exactly two vertices
tie at every breakpoint.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Every edge goes to a sink, to a lower level or down the $c$-chain, so the
game is acyclic and stopping (\Cref{lem:trapchar}), and the values of
$\mathrm{BP}(D)_{\varrho}$ are a backward induction in which every non-sink
contributes a factor $\varrho$: with $f_d:=\val(F_d)$, $g_d:=\val(G_d)$,
$f_0=\varrho^{2}$, $g_0=0$, one has $\val(P_d)=\varrho f_{d-1}$,
$\val(Q_d)=\varrho g_{d-1}$, $\val(B_d)=\varrho\kappa_d$,
$\val(X_d)=\tfrac{\varrho^{2}}2f_{d-1}$,
$\val(Y_d)=\tfrac{\varrho^{2}}2(g_{d-1}+\kappa_d)$, and
$\val(w^{d}_3)=\varrho\kappa_{d-1}$,
$\val(w^{d}_2)=\tfrac{\varrho^{2}}2\kappa_{d-1}$,
$\kappa_d=\tfrac{\varrho^{3}}4\kappa_{d-1}$, $\kappa_1=\tfrac{\varrho}2$. As in
\Cref{thm:fold}, $F_d$ and $G_d$ read the same pair, so
$f_d=\varrho\max(\val X_d,\val Y_d)$, $g_d=\varrho\min(\val X_d,\val Y_d)$
and $\varphi_d=\varrho\,|\val X_d-\val Y_d|=\tfrac{\varrho^{3}}2|\varphi_{d-1}-\kappa_d|$.
With $e_{d-1}/e_d=4/\varrho^{3}$ and $\kappa_d=e_{d-1}/2$,
\[
  \frac{\varphi_d}{e_d}=\frac{\varrho^{3}}2\cdot\frac{e_{d-1}}{e_d}
  \Bigl|\frac{\varphi_{d-1}}{e_{d-1}}-\frac12\Bigr|
  =2\Bigl|\frac{\varphi_{d-1}}{e_{d-1}}-\frac12\Bigr|
  =T\Bigl(\frac{\varphi_{d-1}}{e_{d-1}}\Bigr),
\]
and $\varphi_0/e_0=\varrho^{2}/\varrho=\varrho$. The multiplier $2$ is
independent of $\varrho$ because the fold's contraction $\varrho^{3}/2$ and
the constants' ratio $\varrho^{3}/4$ have been matched; that is the whole
design. $T^{d-1}$ maps each dyadic interval of length $2^{-(d-1)}$ affinely
onto $[0,1]$ with slope $\pm2^{d-1}$, so $T^{d-1}(\varrho)=\tfrac12$ has
exactly the odd multiples of $2^{-d}$ as solutions, each crossed
transversally, and
$\val X_d-\val Y_d=\tfrac{\varrho^{2}}2e_{d-1}\bigl(T^{d-1}(\varrho)-\tfrac12\bigr)$
changes sign exactly there. If $T^{d-1}(\varrho)=\tfrac12$ then
$T^{d}(\varrho)=0$ and $T^{j}(\varrho)=1$ for all $j>d$, so no other level
ties at that $\varrho$; the union of the reversal sets over $d\le D$ is
$\{k2^{-D}:1\le k\le2^{D}-1\}$; and on each of the $2^{D}$ intervals between
consecutive breakpoints the optimal pair is the sign vector
$(\operatorname{sign}(T^{d-1}(\varrho)-\tfrac12))_{d\le D}$, the length-$D$
itinerary of $\varrho$ under $T$, which is injective on those intervals.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:discount-fold}
Three readings. First, this is the fold of \Cref{thm:fold} driven by the
survival probability instead of a frozen payoff, and what the transplant
needs is the right seed, not a new fold: $P_D$ itself, read as a discount
game with $u\to(t_0,t_1)$ unfrozen, already has tent slope exactly $2$
(fold multiplier $\varrho^{2}/2$ against the chain's ratio $\varrho^{2}/4$),
but its seed is $\varphi_0/e_0=\tfrac12$ for every $\varrho$, whose orbit
$\tfrac12,0,1,1,\dots$ ties $F_1,G_1$ identically ($\val X_1=\val Y_1=
\varrho^{2}/4$) and never reverses a level (checked exactly for $D\le7$);
replacing $u$ by the two-vertex chain $u\to(u_2,u_2)$, $u_2\to(t_1,t_1)$
of value $\varrho^{2}$ gives $\varphi_d/e_d=T^{d}(\varrho)$ with
$e_d=\varrho(\varrho^{2}/4)^{d}$ and the same breakpoint set $\{k2^{-D}\}$
on $6D+3$ vertices, i.e.\ $2^{(N-3)/6}-1$ breakpoints (the route's
correctness auditor, correcting the route's account of the transplant;
verified here for $D\le8$, \texttt{scripts/round17-verify/pd\_discount.py}),
so $\mathrm{BP}(D)$'s extra blocks are not needed. Second, the length is
robust: damping the average vertices only, with a two-vertex block in the
$c$-chain ($9D+3$ vertices), gives the same tent identity, and perturbing
the stop payoff to $\varepsilon(v+1)$ makes the start $\varrho=0$ --- where
$w_0=0$ and every controlled vertex is tied --- tie-free and read off in
$O(N)$ while keeping at least $2^{D}-1$ breakpoints (the route's exact
verification at $D\le8$ and $D\le7$). Third, what follows and what does not.
No continuation method that follows the optimal pair of $G_{\varrho}$ from
$\varrho=0$ to $1$ is polynomial; at a breakpoint $\varrho^{\ast}$ the pair
optimal just above it is \emph{an} optimal pair of a derived discounted game
on the tied set $J$ (reward $w_{\varrho^{\ast}}$ read at the chosen
successors, discount $\varrho^{\ast}$, controlled exactly at $J$; the
right derivative of $w_{\varrho}$ is that game's value) --- the standard
sensitive-discount step of Blackwell, Veinott and Miller--Veinott (from
memory, unchecked) --- which names the next pair only when the derived
optimum is unique: it is unique at every breakpoint of $\mathrm{BP}(D)$ and
of the one-Max games below, but the route's auditor built a $14$-vertex
one-player game with $\val(u^{(0)})-\val(u^{(1)})=\tfrac14\varrho^{4}(\varrho-\tfrac12)^{2}$,
at whose breakpoint $\tfrac12$ both actions are optimal in the derived
game. Like $P_D$, $\mathrm{BP}(D)$ is acyclic, hence in
\Cref{thm:avg-acyclic}'s class and solved by backward induction; the
statement constrains one algorithm schema and none of the mechanisms
M1--M6, the improvement rules or the bias homotopy, to which the fold does
not transfer (\Cref{cor:two-homotopies}). Verified from the game in exact arithmetic for
$D\le11$ by the route and for $D\le8$ here
(\texttt{scripts/round17-verify/bp\_verify.py}): the tent identity at every
level, the tied set $\{F_d,G_d\}$ at every $k2^{-D}$, and $2^{D}$ pairwise
distinct pairs, constant inside each interval.
\end{remark}


\subsection{Coupling the two: the transport--slack hybrid}\label{sec:hybrid}

\Cref{rem:transport} records that the slack calculus and the transport
certificate fail in complementary configurations. It is therefore natural
to run them together, and the point of this subsection is that the
combination is not a disjunction of two tests but a genuine feedback loop,
because each mechanism supplies exactly what the other lacks.

What $Q(G)$ lacks is one half of every controlled row. The relations
$\val(v)=\max_i\val(v^{(i)})$ and $\val(v)=\min_i\val(v^{(i)})$ each split
into a convex half and a non-convex half, and \Cref{def:transport} keeps
only the convex halves, $x(v)\ge x(v^{(i)})$ and $x(v)\le x(v^{(i)})$. The
missing halves, $x(v)\le\max_i x(v^{(i)})$ and $x(v)\ge\min_i x(v^{(i)})$,
are not linear constraints and cannot be added to a linear programme --- but
their consequences for \emph{differences} are exactly the $(\mathrm{up})$
and $(\mathrm{down})$ clauses of \Cref{def:slack}. Conversely what
\Cref{def:slack} lacks is a way to solve the average equalities in one
step rather than by iteration; that is what a linear programme does, and
\Cref{lem:transport-exact} is the statement that it does it exactly.

\begin{definition}[The hybrid]\label{def:hybrid}
Let $G$ be an SSG and let $Z_0\subseteq\{w^\ast=0\}$ and
$Z_1\subseteq\{w^\ast=1\}$ be the sets of \Cref{def:simorder}, or any
sound under-approximations of them (soundness below uses only the
inclusions). For a matrix
$\Delta\colon V\times V\to[-1,1]$ put
\[
  K(\Delta)\;:=\;\bigl\{z\in Q(G)\ :\ z(p)-z(q)\le\Delta(p,q)
  \text{ for all }p,q\in V\bigr\},
\]
where as in \Cref{def:transport} every $z\in Q(G)$ has $z(t_0)=0$ and
$z(t_1)=1$. Write $\mathcal L$ for one round of \Cref{def:slack},
$\mathcal C$ for the min-plus closure of \Cref{def:trans-slack}, and
\[
  \mathcal P(\Delta)(x,y)\;:=\;\max\{z(x)-z(y)\ :\ z\in K(\Delta)\} .
\]
The \emph{hybrid} is the iteration $\Delta_0\equiv1$,
\[
  \Delta'_k:=\mathcal C\bigl(\min(\Delta_k,\mathcal L\Delta_k)\bigr),
  \qquad
  \Delta_{k+1}:=\mathcal C\bigl(\min(\Delta'_k,\mathcal P(\Delta'_k))\bigr),
\]
every entry clamped to $[-1,1]$. Call $\Delta$ \emph{closed} if it is
sound, satisfies $\Delta\le\mathcal L\Delta$, $\Delta\le\mathcal C\Delta$
and $\Delta\le\mathcal P(\Delta)$ entrywise. One round is $O(N^{2})$
linear programmes with $N$ variables and $O(N^{2})$ rows, hence
polynomial; \Cref{cor:hybrid-sink} reduces this to $4N$ programmes.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Soundness]\label{thm:hybrid-sound}
For every SSG, every $k$ and all $x,y$,
$\Delta_k(x,y)\ge\val(x)-\val(y)$. No stopping hypothesis is needed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Induction on $k$. The case $k=0$ is trivial, and $\mathcal L$ and
$\mathcal C$ preserve soundness by \Cref{thm:slack-sound} and
\Cref{thm:trans-sound}. For $\mathcal P$: if $\Delta$ is sound then
$\val(p)-\val(q)\le\Delta(p,q)$ for all $p,q$, while
$\val\in Q(G)$ by \Cref{thm:transport-sound}; hence $\val\in K(\Delta)$,
so $K(\Delta)\ne\emptyset$ and
$\mathcal P(\Delta)(x,y)\ge\val(x)-\val(y)$.
\end{proof}

The analysis rests on reading a closed matrix twice: once column by
column, once row by row. Write $\widetilde T$ for the operator that
agrees with $T$ at every non-sink and leaves the two sinks at whatever
values its argument gives them.

\begin{lemma}[Rows and columns]\label{lem:hybrid-fix}
Let $\Delta$ be closed. Then for every $y$ the column
$\varphi_y:=\Delta(\cdot,y)$ satisfies
$\varphi_y\le\widetilde T\varphi_y$ at every non-sink, and for every $x$
the negated row $\psi_x:=-\Delta(x,\cdot)$ satisfies
$\psi_x\ge\widetilde T\psi_x$ at every non-sink. Moreover
\[
  \Delta(t_0,y)\le\Delta(t_1,y)-1
  \qquad\text{and}\qquad
  \Delta(x,t_1)\le\Delta(x,t_0)-1 .
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The $(\mathrm{up})$ clause of \Cref{def:slack} reads
$\Delta(x,y)\le\max_i\Delta(x^{(i)},y)$ for $x\in\Vmax$,
$\le\min_i\Delta(x^{(i)},y)$ for $x\in\Vmin$ and $\le$ the mean for
$x\in\Vavg$; that is precisely
$\varphi_y(x)\le(\widetilde T\varphi_y)(x)$. The $(\mathrm{down})$ clause
reads $\Delta(x,y)\le\min_j\Delta(x,y^{(j)})$ for $y\in\Vmax$,
$\le\max_j$ for $y\in\Vmin$ and $\le$ the mean for $y\in\Vavg$; negating
turns $\min$ into $\max$ and back, giving
$\psi_x(y)\ge(\widetilde T\psi_x)(y)$. For the two displayed
inequalities, the base clauses give $\Delta(t_0,t_1)\le-1$ because
$t_0\in Z_0$ and $t_1\in Z_1$, and the min-plus closure gives
$\Delta(t_0,y)\le\Delta(t_0,t_1)+\Delta(t_1,y)$ and
$\Delta(x,t_1)\le\Delta(x,t_0)+\Delta(t_0,t_1)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Comparison with general sink payoffs]\label{lem:gen-comparison}
Let $G$ be stopping and let $\alpha<\beta$ be reals in $[-1,1]$. Let
$T^{\alpha,\beta}$ agree with $T$ at every non-sink and return $\alpha$
at $t_0$ and $\beta$ at $t_1$. Then $T^{\alpha,\beta}$ has a unique fixed
point in $[-1,1]^{V}$, namely $\alpha+(\beta-\alpha)\val$, and for
$f\in[-1,1]^{V}$,
\[
  f\le T^{\alpha,\beta}f\ \Longrightarrow\ f\le\alpha+(\beta-\alpha)\val,
  \qquad
  f\ge T^{\alpha,\beta}f\ \Longrightarrow\ f\ge\alpha+(\beta-\alpha)\val .
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The affine bijection $A(u):=(u-\alpha)/(\beta-\alpha)$ is increasing, and
$\max$, $\min$ and the mean all commute with increasing affine maps, so
$A\circ T^{\alpha,\beta}=T\circ A$ at every non-sink; at the sinks
$A(\alpha)=0$ and $A(\beta)=1$, which are the sink values of $T$. Hence
$f\le T^{\alpha,\beta}f$ if and only if $Af\le T(Af)$.

It remains to apply \Cref{cor:comparison} to $Af$, which need not lie in
$\mathcal X=[0,1]^{V}$. This is harmless: the proof of
\Cref{thm:contraction} bounds $\lVert T^{N}x-T^{N}y\rVert_\infty$ by
comparing two $N$-stage games that differ only in the terminal payoff
function, and \Cref{lem:absorption} is strategy-independent, so the
estimate holds verbatim for any two bounded terminal payoffs, in
particular on $[-M,M]^{V}$ for every $M$. Consequently $T$ has a unique
fixed point in $[-M,M]^{V}$ for every $M\ge1$, necessarily $\val$, and
every orbit converges to it; the monotone argument of
\Cref{cor:comparison} then gives $Af\le\val$ from $Af\le T(Af)$ and
$Af\ge\val$ from $Af\ge T(Af)$. Transporting back through $A$ gives both
implications.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Completeness: the polytope collapses]\label{thm:hybrid-complete}
Let $G$ be stopping and let $\Delta$ be closed. Then
\[
  K(\Delta)=\{\val\}
  \qquad\text{and}\qquad
  \Delta(x,y)=\val(x)-\val(y)\ \text{ for all }x,y .
\]
In particular every pair is decided. Since the sequence $(\Delta_k)$ of
\Cref{def:hybrid} is nonincreasing and bounded below by
$\val(x)-\val(y)$, its limit is closed, so the hybrid converges to the
exact differences on every stopping game.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $L(v):=\min\{z(v):z\in K(\Delta)\}$ and
$U(v):=\max\{z(v):z\in K(\Delta)\}$. These are attained, and
$\val\in K(\Delta)$ by soundness, so $L\le\val\le U$ with
$L(t_0)=U(t_0)=0$ and $L(t_1)=U(t_1)=1$. Closedness under $\mathcal P$
gives the four inequalities
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hyb-sink}
  \Delta(t_0,y)\le-L(y),\quad
  \Delta(t_1,y)\le1-L(y),\quad
  \Delta(x,t_0)\le U(x),\quad
  \Delta(x,t_1)\le U(x)-1 ,
\end{equation}
since, for instance, $\mathcal P(\Delta)(t_0,y)=\max\{-z(y)\}=-L(y)$.

\emph{Step 1: $\Delta(x,y)\le\val(x)-L(y)$.} Fix $y$ and apply
\Cref{lem:hybrid-fix}: $\varphi_y\le\widetilde T\varphi_y$ with sink
values $\alpha:=\Delta(t_0,y)$ and $\beta:=\Delta(t_1,y)$, and
$\alpha\le\beta-1<\beta$. So $\varphi_y\le T^{\alpha,\beta}\varphi_y$ and
\Cref{lem:gen-comparison} gives
$\varphi_y(x)\le\alpha\bigl(1-\val(x)\bigr)+\beta\val(x)$. That
expression is nondecreasing in $\alpha$ and in $\beta$ because
$0\le\val(x)\le1$, so by the first two inequalities of
\eqref{eq:hyb-sink},
\[
  \Delta(x,y)\;\le\;-L(y)\bigl(1-\val(x)\bigr)+\bigl(1-L(y)\bigr)\val(x)
  \;=\;\val(x)-L(y).
\]

\emph{Step 2: $\Delta(x,y)\le U(x)-\val(y)$.} Fix $x$ and apply
\Cref{lem:hybrid-fix} to $\psi_x=-\Delta(x,\cdot)$, whose sink values are
$a:=-\Delta(x,t_0)$ and $b:=-\Delta(x,t_1)$, with $a\le b-1<b$ by the
second display of that lemma. By \Cref{lem:gen-comparison},
$\psi_x(y)\ge a(1-\val(y))+b\val(y)$, that is
\[
  \Delta(x,y)\;\le\;\Delta(x,t_0)\bigl(1-\val(y)\bigr)+\Delta(x,t_1)\val(y)
  \;\le\;U(x)\bigl(1-\val(y)\bigr)+\bigl(U(x)-1\bigr)\val(y)
  \;=\;U(x)-\val(y),
\]
using the last two inequalities of \eqref{eq:hyb-sink}.

\emph{Step 3: $U=\val$.} Every $z\in K(\Delta)$ obeys
$z(x)-z(t_0)\le\Delta(x,t_0)$, i.e.\ $z(x)\le\Delta(x,t_0)$, so
$U(x)\le\Delta(x,t_0)$. Step 1 with $y=t_0$ gives
$\Delta(x,t_0)\le\val(x)-L(t_0)=\val(x)$. Hence $U\le\val$, and $U\ge\val$
was noted above.

\emph{Step 4: $L=\val$.} Every $z\in K(\Delta)$ obeys
$z(t_1)-z(y)\le\Delta(t_1,y)$, i.e.\ $z(y)\ge1-\Delta(t_1,y)$, so
$L(y)\ge1-\Delta(t_1,y)$. Step 2 with $x=t_1$ gives
$\Delta(t_1,y)\le U(t_1)-\val(y)=1-\val(y)$. Hence $L\ge\val$, and
$L\le\val$ was noted above.

\emph{Step 5.} By Steps 3 and 4 every $z\in K(\Delta)$ satisfies
$\val=L\le z\le U=\val$, so $K(\Delta)=\{\val\}$ and therefore
$\mathcal P(\Delta)(x,y)=\val(x)-\val(y)$. Closedness gives
$\Delta(x,y)\le\val(x)-\val(y)$ and soundness the reverse.

For the last sentence, $(\Delta_k)$ is nonincreasing and bounded below, so
it converges; $\mathcal L$, $\mathcal C$ and $\mathcal P$ are continuous
in $\Delta$ (the first two visibly; for the third, $K(\Delta_k)$ is a nonincreasing
sequence of nonempty compact sets --- nonempty by soundness --- whose
intersection is $K(\Delta_\infty)$, so the maximum of a continuous
function over $K(\Delta_k)$ converges to its maximum over
$K(\Delta_\infty)$), so the limit is a closed matrix.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Only the sink rows are used]\label{cor:hybrid-sink}
\Cref{thm:hybrid-complete} uses the linear programme only through the
$4N$ values $\mathcal P(\Delta)(t_i,y)$ and $\mathcal P(\Delta)(x,t_i)$,
$i\in\{0,1\}$ --- Step~5 as written invokes closedness at a general pair,
but in the sink-only variant the same conclusion comes from the min-plus
closure that \Cref{def:hybrid} applies:
$\Delta(x,y)\le\Delta(x,t_0)+\Delta(t_0,y)\le U(x)-L(y)=\val(x)-\val(y)$.
Hence the variant of \Cref{def:hybrid} that applies
$\mathcal P$ only at pairs containing a sink is already complete on
stopping games, and one of its rounds costs $4N$ linear programmes rather
than $N^{2}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:hybrid-box}
\Cref{cor:hybrid-sink} identifies what the linear programme contributes.
The four quantities it needs are exactly the box
$\bigl[L,U\bigr]$ of $K(\Delta)$, since
$\mathcal P(\Delta)(x,t_0)=U(x)$ and
$\mathcal P(\Delta)(t_1,y)=1-L(y)$. So the hybrid is a
\emph{box-propagation loop}: from the current difference bounds it forms
the polytope $K(\Delta)$, reads off its box, feeds the box back as four
families of difference facts, and lets the slack clauses propagate.
\Cref{thm:hybrid-complete} says the only fixed point of that loop on a
stopping game is the exact one. What the theorem does \emph{not} give is a
rate, and by \Cref{thm:decide-one-bit} a polynomial bound on the number of
rounds before some value-distinguishing controlled vertex is decided would
put \textsc{SSG-Value} in $\mathsf P$. That is the exact form in which
this mechanism leaves the problem open.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[It disposes of every stall recorded above]\label{prop:hybrid-decides}
Let $\Delta_k$ be as in \Cref{def:hybrid} and let a pair be
\emph{decided at round $k$} if $\Delta_k$ is negative at it.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item On $G_8$ of \Cref{prop:simorder-stalls} the pair $(4,1)$ is decided
      at round $1$, against $7$ rounds for \Cref{def:slack} and $6$ for
      \Cref{def:trans-slack}.
\item On $H_m$ of \Cref{cor:slack-stalls} the pair $(c_1,h)$ is decided at
      round $1$ for $m=3,4,5,6$, against $14,38,100,249$ rounds for
      \Cref{def:slack} and $9,13,17,21$ for \Cref{def:trans-slack}.
\item On $S$ of \Cref{prop:transport-stalls}, where \Cref{def:transport}
      stalls outright, the pair is decided at round $2$; on $S_r$ of
      \Cref{thm:transport-barrier} it is decided at round $2$ for
      $r=2,\dots,10$, against $12,25,52,104,210,\dots$ rounds for
      \Cref{def:slack} and $8,11,15,18,22,\dots$ for
      \Cref{def:trans-slack}.
\item On $R$ of \Cref{prop:own-stall}, the one genuine decision stall of
      \Cref{def:transport} recorded here, with five controlled vertices,
      the own-successor test fires at the Min vertex $6$ at round $1$ and
      at the other two value-distinguishing vertices, $1$ and $4$, at
      round $2$ --- with or without the $Z_0$/$Z_1$ seed, and in the
      sink-only variant alike.
\end{enumerate}
All of these were computed in exact rational arithmetic, with soundness
re-verified against the true values at every round; on these particular
instances the sink-only variant of \Cref{cor:hybrid-sink} returns the same
counts, which \Cref{rem:hybrid-sink-slower} shows is not a general phenomenon.

The counts above are for the pair test, which is what the earlier propositions
of this section measure; under the operative own-successor test of
\Cref{rem:own-successor} the instances of (a)--(c) are decided at round $1$
and $R$ at round $2$ (its vertex $6$ at round $1$, its vertices $1$ and $4$
at round $2$). \Cref{prop:hybrid-onectrl} says how little either statement means:
every instance of (a)--(c) has exactly one controlled vertex; $R$ has five
and is decided at round two all the same.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}[One controlled vertex is always exact at round two]\label{prop:hybrid-onectrl}
Let $G$ be stopping with $|\Vmax|+|\Vmin|=1$. Then $K(\Delta_2)=\{\val\}$ and
$\Delta_2(x,y)=\val(x)-\val(y)$ for all $x,y$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Take $\Vmax=\{v\}$, $\Vmin=\emptyset$; the other case reverses every
inequality. Making $v$ and the sinks absorbing leaves an average chain, which
is absorbing because $G$ is stopping, so every $z\in Q(G)$ has
$z(x)=\theta_x t+\rho_x$ with $t:=z(v)$, $\theta_x$ the probability of reaching
$v$ before a sink and $\rho_x$ that of reaching $t_1$ first. Both successors of
$v$ have $\theta<1$: if $\theta_{v^{(i)}}=1$ then the positional strategy
$\sigma(v)=v^{(i)}$ returns to $v$ almost surely without ever reaching a sink,
contradicting stopping. The only rows of $Q(G)$ constraining $t$ from below are
the two Max rows $t\ge\theta_{i}t+\rho_{i}$, so $Q(G)$ is the segment
$[t_-,t_+]$ with $t_-=\max_i\rho_i/(1-\theta_i)$, and $t_-=\val(v)$ because
that is the least fixed point of $t\mapsto\max_i(\theta_it+\rho_i)$
(\Cref{thm:lfp-general}). Relabel so that $A$ attains the maximum, and put
$\gamma:=\val(A)-\val(B)\ge0$. Since $\val\in K(\Delta_1)\subseteq Q(G)$, any
linear function of $t$ with nonpositive slope attains its maximum over
$K(\Delta_1)$ at $t=t_-$, that is at $\val$. If $\theta_A\le\theta_B$ then
$\Delta_1(A,B)=\gamma$ exactly, so the $(\mathrm{up})$ clause at $v$ with
$y=B$ gives $\Delta_2(v,B)\le\max(\gamma,0)=\gamma$, and the constraint
$z(v)-z(B)\le\gamma$ reads $(1-\theta_B)t-\rho_B\le(1-\theta_B)t_--\rho_B$,
i.e.\ $t\le t_-$. If $\theta_A>\theta_B$ then $\Delta_1(B,A)=-\gamma\le0$ and
the $(\mathrm{up})$ clause at $v$ with $y=A$ gives
$\Delta_2(v,A)\le\max(0,-\gamma)=0$, and $z(v)-z(A)\le0$ reads
$(1-\theta_A)t\le\rho_A$, i.e.\ again $t\le t_-$. Either way
$K(\Delta_2)=\{\val\}$, and \Cref{thm:hybrid-complete}'s last step applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:hybrid-sink-slower}
\Cref{cor:hybrid-sink} says the sink-only variant is \emph{complete}; it does
not say it is as fast, and it is not. On $CC(1,2)$ of
\Cref{thm:hybrid-lower} the full hybrid decides at round $3$ and the sink-only
variant at round $5$; on the nine-vertex game $W$ of \Cref{rem:hybrid-rate} the
full hybrid collapses the box at round $3$ and the sink-only variant at round
$7$. The instances of \Cref{prop:hybrid-decides} do not separate them because,
by \Cref{prop:hybrid-onectrl}, they are all decided at round two by anything
that consults the programme at the sinks at all.
\end{remark}

\begin{proof}
By computation from \Cref{def:hybrid}; the values of the instances are
those recorded in \Cref{prop:simorder-stalls}, \Cref{thm:vi-lower},
\Cref{prop:transport-stalls} and \Cref{thm:transport-barrier}. The
mechanism behind (c) is worth recording, since it is what
\Cref{thm:transport-barrier} shows cannot happen for
\Cref{def:transport} alone. On $S_r$ the polytope $Q(S_r)$ is a segment
parametrised by $t=x(v)\in[\tfrac12,1]$, and the certificate needs
$t$ localised to within $2^{-\Theta(N)}$. Round $1$ of the hybrid adds no
slack facts beyond the bases, so its linear programme returns exactly the
two separators of \Cref{thm:transport-barrier}; but one of them,
$\operatorname{Sep}(b_0,a)=2^{-r-2}$, is \emph{exact}, and it is recorded
as the difference fact $\Delta_1(a,b_0)\le2^{-r-2}$. At round $2$ the
$(\mathrm{up})$ clause at the Max vertex $v$ turns this into
$\Delta_2(v,b_0)\le\max\bigl(\Delta_1(a,b_0),\Delta_1(b_0,b_0)\bigr)=2^{-r-2}$,
and the constraint $x(v)-x(b_0)\le2^{-r-2}$ collapses the segment to the
single point $t=\tfrac12$, because $x^{(t)}(v)-x^{(t)}(b_0)=2^{-r}(t-\tfrac14)$.
The polytope having collapsed, the second separator is now $-2^{-r-2}<0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:hybrid-where}
\Cref{prop:hybrid-decides}(c) is the general pattern: the direction in
which the transport certificate is \emph{tight} is fed to the slack
clause at a controlled vertex, and the resulting difference constraint
shrinks the polytope in the direction in which the certificate was loose.
Neither mechanism does this alone. It also locates where a stall would
have to sit. \Cref{thm:slack-barrier} and \Cref{thm:seeded-barrier}
defeat the slack calculus on two disjoint parts $A,B$ closed under
non-sink successors and avoiding $Z_0\cup Z_1$; but if such a part
consists only of average vertices then it satisfies the hypothesis of
\Cref{lem:transport-exact}, so the linear programme pins $x=\val$ on it
at the first round and no pair drawn from it can witness a stall. Hence
any configuration defeating the hybrid in the sense of those barriers
must place a \emph{controlled} vertex inside $A$ or inside $B$ --- which
is exactly the configuration that defeats \Cref{def:transport}, and
exactly the one in which the slack clauses at that vertex have something
to say. We have not been able to satisfy both demands at once, and we do
not claim it is impossible.
\end{remark}


\begin{proposition}[The rate is a contraction question]\label{prop:hybrid-rate}
Let $G$ be stopping with $a=|\Vavg|$, let $\Delta'_k$ be as in
\Cref{def:hybrid} and write
\[
  L_k(v):=\min\{z(v):z\in K(\Delta'_k)\},\qquad
  U_k(v):=\max\{z(v):z\in K(\Delta'_k)\},\qquad
  \omega_k:=\max_{v\in V}\bigl(U_k(v)-L_k(v)\bigr)
\]
for the box of the polytope over which round $k+1$ optimises. Then
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2.2em]
\item $\Delta_{k+1}(x,y)\le U_k(x)-L_k(y)$ for all $x,y\in V$;
\item if $\omega_k<2^{-(a+1)}$ then $\Delta_{k+1}(x,y)<0$ for every pair with
      $\val(x)<\val(y)$; in particular every value-distinguishing controlled
      vertex is decided at round $k+1$;
\item consequently, if $\omega_k\le C\rho^{k}$ for all $k$ and some constants
      $C\ge1$ and $\rho=\rho(G)<1$, then every value-distinguishing pair is
      decided within $\lceil (a+1+\log_2C)/\log_2(1/\rho)\rceil+1$ rounds.
\end{enumerate}
The hypothesis of (c) is stated for the sequence and not round by round, and
that is necessary: \Cref{rem:hybrid-rate} exhibits a stopping game on which one
round leaves $\omega_k$ exactly unchanged.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(a) The transport step of \Cref{def:hybrid} gives
$\Delta_{k+1}(x,t_0)\le\mathcal P(\Delta'_k)(x,t_0)=\max\{z(x)-z(t_0)\}=U_k(x)$
and $\Delta_{k+1}(t_0,y)\le\max\{z(t_0)-z(y)\}=-L_k(y)$, both maxima over
$K(\Delta'_k)$; the min-plus closure that follows it gives
$\Delta_{k+1}(x,y)\le\Delta_{k+1}(x,t_0)+\Delta_{k+1}(t_0,y)$.

(b) By \Cref{thm:hybrid-sound}, $\val\in K(\Delta'_k)$, so
$L_k\le\val\le U_k$ and therefore $U_k(x)\le\val(x)+\omega_k$ and
$L_k(y)\ge\val(y)-\omega_k$. With (a),
\[
  \Delta_{k+1}(x,y)\;\le\;\val(x)-\val(y)+2\omega_k .
\]
Let $(\sigma^\ast,\tau^\ast)$ be an optimal pair. By
\Cref{thm:stopping-determinacy} $\val=v_{\sigma^\ast,\tau^\ast}$ is the
solution of a linear system whose matrix is $I-A_{\sigma^\ast,\tau^\ast}$, so
by \Cref{lem:denominator-sharp} every coordinate of $\val$ has denominator
dividing one integer $D\le2^{a}$. Hence two distinct coordinates differ by at
least $2^{-a}$, and $\val(x)<\val(y)$ forces
$\val(x)-\val(y)\le-2^{-a}<-2\omega_k$.

(c) By (b) it suffices that $C\rho^{k}<2^{-(a+1)}$, i.e.\ that
$k\log_2(1/\rho)>a+1+\log_2C$.
\end{proof}




\begin{lemma}[The hybrid is a cutting-plane iteration]\label{lem:hybrid-cutting}
For a nonempty compact $P\subseteq\mathcal X$ put
$\Lambda(P)(x,y):=\max_{z\in P}(z(x)-z(y))$. Then $\Lambda(P)$ satisfies the
triangle inequality, and $\Lambda(K(M))\le M$ pointwise for every sound $M$.
Consequently, in \Cref{def:hybrid} both the outer minimum with $\Delta'_k$ and
the outer min-plus closure are redundant, and writing $Q^H_k:=K(\Delta'_k)$ one
has $\Delta_{k+1}=\Lambda(Q^H_k)$ for every $k\ge0$, with
\[
  Q^H_{k+1}\;=\;K\bigl(\min\bigl(\Lambda(Q^H_k),\,
  \mathcal L\Lambda(Q^H_k)\bigr)\bigr).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $z\in P$ attaining $\Lambda(P)(x,w)$ one has
$z(x)-z(w)=(z(x)-z(y))+(z(y)-z(w))\le\Lambda(P)(x,y)+\Lambda(P)(y,w)$, which is
the triangle inequality. Every $z\in K(M)$ obeys $z(x)-z(y)\le M(x,y)$ by
definition, so $\Lambda(K(M))\le M$. The two redundancies follow: the transport
step already returns something $\le\Delta'_k$, and its output is a $\Lambda$,
hence already min-plus closed.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Convex certificates bound the hybrid from below]\label{thm:hybrid-convex-barrier}
Let $G$ be an SSG and let $P_0\supseteq P_1\supseteq\cdots\supseteq P_K$
($K\le\infty$) be nonempty compact convex subsets of $Q(G)$ such that for
every $k<K$ each clause of \Cref{def:slack}, evaluated at the matrix
$\Lambda(P_k)$, is at least $\Lambda(P_{k+1})$ entrywise. Then
$\Delta_k\ge\Lambda(P_k)$ for every $k\le K$ (the applications below use
finite chains, which end when $M_k<0$). In
particular the hybrid decides no pair $(x,y)$ with $\Lambda(P_k)(x,y)\ge0$ at
any round $\le k$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Induction. For $k=0$, $P_0\subseteq\mathcal X$ gives $\Lambda(P_0)\le1=\Delta_0$.
Suppose $\Delta_k\ge\Lambda(P_k)$. The clauses of \Cref{def:slack} are monotone
in the matrix, so $\mathcal L\Delta_k\ge\mathcal L\Lambda(P_k)\ge\Lambda(P_{k+1})$
by hypothesis, and $\Delta_k\ge\Lambda(P_k)\ge\Lambda(P_{k+1})$ by nesting;
hence $\min(\Delta_k,\mathcal L\Delta_k)\ge\Lambda(P_{k+1})$. Since
$\Lambda(P_{k+1})$ satisfies the triangle inequality
(\Cref{lem:hybrid-cutting}), the min-plus closure cannot fall below it, so
$\Delta'_k\ge\Lambda(P_{k+1})$. Finally every $z\in P_{k+1}$ lies in $Q(G)$ and
satisfies $z(x)-z(y)\le\Lambda(P_{k+1})(x,y)\le\Delta'_k(x,y)$, so
$P_{k+1}\subseteq K(\Delta'_k)$ and therefore
$\mathcal P(\Delta'_k)=\Lambda(K(\Delta'_k))\ge\Lambda(P_{k+1})$. Taking the
minimum and closing again preserves the bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:ratio-sign}
For nonempty compact $P\subseteq\mathcal X$ and all $x,y$:
$\Lambda_{\times}(P)(x,y)\le1$ if and only if $\Lambda(P)(x,y)\le0$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $\Lambda(P)(x,y)\le0$ then $z(x)\le z(y)$ for every $z\in P$; in
particular $z(y)=0$ forces $z(x)=0$, so the $+\infty$ case does not occur
and every ratio with positive denominator is at most $1$. Conversely
$\Lambda_\times(P)(x,y)\le1$ excludes the $+\infty$ case, so $z(y)=0$
implies $z(x)=0$, and where $z(y)>0$ the ratio bound gives
$z(x)\le z(y)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Convex certificates bound the calculi in both registers]\label{thm:convex-barrier-both}
Let $G$ be an SSG, $Z_0,Z_1$ as in \Cref{def:simorder}, $L_Z:=\bbone_{Z_1}$,
$U_Z:=\bbone-\bbone_{Z_0}$ the \emph{free bracket}, and for a nonempty
compact $P\subseteq\mathcal X$ put
\[
  \Lambda_{\times}(P)(x,y):=
  \begin{cases}
    0,&z(x)=0\text{ for all }z\in P,\\
    +\infty,&z(y)=0<z(x)\text{ for some }z\in P,\\
    \max\{z(x)/z(y):z\in P,\ z(y)>0\},&\text{otherwise,}
  \end{cases}
\]
the multiplicative counterpart of $\Lambda(P)$. Consider any run that
starts from $\Delta_0(x,y)=\mathrm{clamp}_{[-1,1]}(U_Z(x)-L_Z(y))$,
$R_0\equiv+\infty$ and applies, in any order, the operators
\textup{(D1)} $\Delta\mapsto\min(\Delta,\mathcal L\Delta)$, one round of
\Cref{def:slack}; \textup{(D2)} the min-plus closure of
\Cref{def:trans-slack}; \textup{(D3)} $\Delta\mapsto\min(\Delta,\Lambda(K_Z(\Delta)))$,
the transport step over $K_Z(\Delta):=\{z\in Q(G;L_Z,U_Z):z(x)-z(y)\le\Delta(x,y)\}$;
\textup{(D4)} $R\mapsto\min(R,\mathcal R_\times R)$, one round of
\Cref{def:ratio}; \textup{(D5)} rounding every entry of $\Delta$ or $R$ up
onto a finite grid; \textup{(D6)} the entrywise minimum with the state of
another such run on the same game (combination, and seeding one calculus
with another's output; the other run's applications are counted);
\textup{(D7)} $\Delta(x,y)\leftarrow0$ where $R(x,y)\le1$ and
$R(x,y)\leftarrow1$ where $\Delta(x,y)\le0$ (order transfer). The slack
calculus, its closure, the transport step, the hybrid of \Cref{def:hybrid}
and the ratio calculus are such runs, $k$ rounds costing $O(k)$
applications. Let $P_0\supseteq P_1\supseteq\cdots\supseteq P_K$ be nonempty compact
convex subsets of $Q(G;L_Z,U_Z)$ such that for every $k<K$
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},leftmargin=2.6em]
\item every clause of \Cref{def:slack} evaluated at $\Lambda(P_k)$ is at
  least $\Lambda(P_{k+1})$ entrywise, and
\item every clause of \Cref{def:ratio} evaluated at
  $\Lambda_{\times}(P_k)$ is at least $\Lambda_{\times}(P_{k+1})$
  entrywise.
\end{enumerate}
Then every such run has, after $k\le K$ operator applications, a state
with $\Delta\ge\Lambda(P_k)$ and $R\ge\Lambda_{\times}(P_k)$; the state is
moreover sound, so each entry is also at least $w^\ast(x)-w^\ast(y)$,
resp.\ $w^\ast(x)/w^\ast(y)$, and a test is read against the larger of
the two bounds. Consequently, within $K$ applications it decides
$v\in\Vmax$ only if one of the four quantities
$\Lambda(P_K)(v,v^{(0)})$, $\Lambda(P_K)(v,v^{(1)})$,
$\Lambda(P_K)(v^{(0)},v^{(1)})$, $\Lambda(P_K)(v^{(1)},v^{(0)})$ is
$\le 0$, or one of $\Lambda(P_K)(v^{(0)},v)$, $\Lambda(P_K)(v^{(1)},v)$
is $<0$, or the corresponding multiplicative quantity is $\le1$
resp.\ $<1$; dually at $\Vmin$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Induction on the number $k$ of applications, with invariant
$\Delta\ge\Lambda(P_k)$ and $R\ge\Lambda_{\times}(P_k)$; both sequences
are nonincreasing in $k$ because the $P_k$ are nested.

\emph{Start.} Every $z\in P_0\subseteq Q(G;L_Z,U_Z)$ has
$L_Z\le z\le U_Z$, so $z(x)-z(y)\le U_Z(x)-L_Z(y)$, and $\Lambda$ is
$\ge-1$ on $\mathcal X$, so $\Lambda(P_0)\le\Delta_0$;
$R_0\equiv+\infty\ge\Lambda_\times(P_0)$.

\textup{(D1)}: the clauses are monotone in the matrix, so
$\mathcal L\Delta\ge\mathcal L\Lambda(P_k)\ge\Lambda(P_{k+1})$ by (i), and
$\Delta\ge\Lambda(P_k)\ge\Lambda(P_{k+1})$.
\textup{(D2)}: $\Lambda(P_{k+1})$ obeys the triangle inequality
(\Cref{lem:hybrid-cutting}), so the min-plus closure of a matrix
$\ge\Lambda(P_{k+1})$ stays $\ge\Lambda(P_{k+1})$.
\textup{(D3)}: every $z\in P_{k+1}$ lies in $Q(G;L_Z,U_Z)$ and satisfies
$z(x)-z(y)\le\Lambda(P_{k+1})(x,y)\le\Delta(x,y)$, so
$P_{k+1}\subseteq K(\Delta)$ and $\Lambda(K(\Delta))\ge\Lambda(P_{k+1})$.
\textup{(D4)}: as \textup{(D1)}, with (ii).
\textup{(D5)}: rounding up only raises entries.
\textup{(D6)}: the cost counts the whole computation, so the run
supplying $(\Delta',R')$ has made at most $k$ applications and
$\Delta'\ge\Lambda(P_k)\ge\Lambda(P_{k+1})$, likewise for $R'$; a minimum
of two such matrices is again $\ge\Lambda(P_{k+1})$.
\textup{(D7)}: if $R(x,y)\le1$ then $\Lambda_\times(P_k)(x,y)\le1$, so
$\Lambda(P_k)(x,y)\le0$ by \Cref{lem:ratio-sign} and setting $\Delta(x,y):=0$
preserves the invariant at that entry; symmetrically in the other
direction.

The last sentence is the reporting rule --- the three readings of
\Cref{rem:own-successor} --- applied to a state satisfying the invariant.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:convex-barrier-both}
\Cref{thm:convex-barrier-both} is \Cref{thm:hybrid-convex-barrier} with the
free seed, the ratio calculus and every combination of the mechanisms
adjoined (the round-$17$ oracle-barrier route); its additive half restricted
to \Cref{def:hybrid} is that theorem verbatim. What it does not cover is
any operator that evaluates a strategy --- $M(p,k)$ of \Cref{def:seeded},
all-switches, \Cref{def:bsi} --- and any coinductive reading over a grid
(\Cref{def:simorder} is one, see the remark after \Cref{def:slack}), whose
iteration starts unsound. Two consequences. A stationary certificate ---
$P$ with $\mathcal L\Lambda(P)\ge\Lambda(P)$ --- bounds nothing for ever:
taking the constant chain gives $\Delta_k\ge\Lambda(P)$ for the slack
calculus at every $k$, while \Cref{thm:slack-vi-upper} and
\Cref{thm:contraction} give $\Delta_{2k}(x,y)\to w^\ast(x)-w^\ast(y)$, so
$\Lambda(P)(x,y)\le w^\ast(x)-w^\ast(y)$ and every lower bound of this
kind is a rate, as \Cref{rem:frontier-moved} says. The multiplicative cases below use that $\max_{z\in P}z(x)/z(y)$ over a
polytope $P$ on which $z(y)>0$ is attained at a vertex: it is the least
$t$ with $\max_{z\in P}(z(x)-t\,z(y))\le0$, and $z(x)-t\,z(y)$ is affine,
so that maximum sits at a vertex for every $t$. The min-times closure of
\Cref{prop:ratio-closure} is not among the operators above; on the family
of \Cref{sec:bc} it was measured silent as well (the route's auditor,
$16$ rounds on $\mathrm{BC}(2,5)$).
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:hybrid-barrier-lift}
\Cref{thm:slack-barrier} and \Cref{thm:seeded-barrier} do \emph{not} lift to the
hybrid, and $H_m$ shows it: there $A=\{h\}$ and $B=\{c_1,\dots,c_{2m}\}$ satisfy
every hypothesis of \Cref{thm:slack-barrier}, yet
$\Delta_1(h,c_1)=-2^{-(m+1)}<0$ by \Cref{prop:hybrid-decides}(b), while
$u_k(h)-l_k(c_1)>0$ for every $k<\ln2\cdot2^{m-2}$. The reason is
\Cref{lem:transport-exact}: an all-average part closed under non-sink successors
is pinned by the programme at once, so the box certificate
$\{l_k\le z\le u_k\}$ is far too weak to bound the hybrid.
\Cref{thm:hybrid-convex-barrier} is the correct generalisation --- the box is
replaced by an arbitrary shrinking chain of convex certificates --- and it is
what the next theorem uses. \Cref{cor:separable}(d),(e) give the structural
form of the same fact: no separable certificate bounds the hybrid on any
stopping game, and what the convex certificates change is that their
diagonal vanishes.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[The hybrid needs exponentially many rounds]\label{thm:hybrid-lower}
For $L\ge1$ and $m\ge2$ let $CC(L,m)$ be the SSG with
$\Vmax=\{v_1,v_2\}$, $\Vmin=\emptyset$ and average vertices: one vertex
$e\to(t_1,t_0)$; a chain $d_1,\dots,d_m$ with $d_1\to(t_0,d_2)$,
$d_j\to(t_1,d_{j+1})$ for $2\le j\le m-1$ and $d_m\to(t_1,t_0)$; and four chains
$\mathrm{ch}(l,b,z)$ consisting of $p_1,\dots,p_l$ with $p_j\to(b,p_{j+1})$ for
$j<l$ and $p_l\to(b,z)$, namely
\[
  A_1=\mathrm{ch}(L,v_2,e),\quad B_1=\mathrm{ch}(2L,v_1,d_1),\quad
  A_2=\mathrm{ch}(L,v_1,e),\quad B_2=\mathrm{ch}(2L,v_2,d_1),
\]
with $v_1\to(A_1,B_1)$ and $v_2\to(A_2,B_2)$, where a chain name used as a
successor means its first vertex. Then $CC(L,m)$ is a stopping one-player SSG
with $N=6L+m+5$,
\[
  \val(v_i)=\val(A_i)=\tfrac12,\qquad
  \val(B_i)=\tfrac12-\gamma\ \ (\gamma:=2^{-2L-m}),\qquad
  \val(d_1)=\tfrac12-2^{-m},
\]
so both Max vertices are value-distinguishing and both must move to the
\emph{cross} chain. Put $u:=2^{-L}$, $\lambda:=1-u$, $M_0:=\tfrac12$ and
$M_{k+1}:=\lambda M_k-\gamma/u$, and let $P_k$ be the image of
\[
  \Xi_k:=\{\xi\in[0,M_k]^2:\ \xi_1\ge\lambda\xi_2,\ \xi_2\ge\lambda\xi_1\}
\]
under $\xi\mapsto\val+\bigl(x\mapsto\langle\theta_x,\xi\rangle\bigr)$, where
$z(x)=\langle\theta_x,(z(v_1),z(v_2))\rangle+\rho_x$ is the affine form of
$Q(CC(L,m))$. Then $(P_k)$ satisfies the hypotheses of
\Cref{thm:hybrid-convex-barrier}, and
$\Lambda(P_k)(B_i,A_i)=-\gamma+2u(1-u)M_k$. Consequently
$\Delta_k(B_i,A_i)\ge0$ --- the hybrid does not order the two successors of
either Max vertex against each other --- at every round $k$ with
$M_k>\gamma/\bigl(2u(1-u)\bigr)$, and since
$M_k=\lambda^{k}(\tfrac12+2^{-m})-2^{-m}$ this holds for all
\[
  k\;<\;\frac{(m-\log_23)\ln2}{\ln(1/\lambda)},
  \qquad \ln(1/\lambda)\le\frac1{2^{L}-1},
\]
so for fixed $m$ that number of rounds is $2^{\Omega(N)}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}[What \Cref{thm:hybrid-lower} does not say]\label{rem:hybrid-lower-not-a-refutation}
It is not a refutation of the hybrid as a decision rule, and we state that
plainly because we first read it as one. By \Cref{rem:own-successor} the rule
does not need the entry $\Delta_k(B_i,A_i)$; it needs to know which successor of
$v_i$ is optimal, and $\Delta_k(B_i,v_i)<0$ supplies that, since
$\val(v_i)=\max(\val(A_i),\val(B_i))$ forces the maximum onto $A_i$. On
$CC(L,m)$ that entry is already negative at round one:
$\theta_{B_1}-\theta_{v_1}=(-2^{-2L},0)$ gives $h_k$ of it equal to $0$, so
$\Lambda(P_k)(B_1,v_1)=-\gamma$ for every $k$, and the certificate is
\emph{consistent} with the vertex being decided immediately. It is: we computed
$\Delta_1(B_1,v_1)=-2^{-4},-2^{-6},-2^{-6}$ on $CC(1,2)$, $CC(2,2)$ and
$CC(1,4)$, deciding both Max vertices at round one.

So what \Cref{thm:hybrid-lower} exhibits is a family on which one natural
