switching rule at all (\Cref{prop:freeze-escapes}), but only because the
padding was too short --- lengthening each of its gadgets to $k+1$ links
blinds every rule that frees at most $k$ average vertices, so the
hierarchy is covered for every window of size $O(N^{\alpha})$ with
$\alpha<1$, though not for windows proportional to $N$
(\Cref{thm:window-barrier}). Finally, the one general
hammer for minimising a polynomial-time computable function over a strategy
cube is submodularity, and the objective here is not submodular under any
orientation of either cube, on a five-vertex witness
(\Cref{lem:readonce,prop:no-submodular}).

\paragraph{The all-switches rule, and the pivot.} Every all-switches run
of every stopping game, degenerate or not, is the bottom-antipodal walk
of an acyclic unique sink orientation of the Max cube
(\Cref{prop:allsw-auso,thm:flat-resolution,cor:ceiling-general}), so its
length is at most $h^{\ast}(m)=1,2,4,7,12$ for $m\le5$
(\Cref{prop:auso-census,prop:hstar-five}); conversely
$h^{\ast}(m+2)\ge2h^{\ast}(m)+2$ by a height-doubling blow-up proved here
and, in its combinatorial clauses, machine-checked (\Cref{thm:blowup}), so the ceiling is exponential
and the question is realisability at polynomial size
(\Cref{prop:auso-size}). The height-seven orbit is realised by a
two-player game on $97$ vertices (\Cref{prop:auso-seven}) and by no
nondegenerate one-player game --- one-player orientations are
Holt--Klee, \Cref{prop:oneplayer-lp,cor:seven-two-player} --- and no
one-player game has a run realising it, with or without ties
(\Cref{thm:no-seven}); a zero-tie timer wakes within $|\Vmax|$ rounds,
so one-shot zero-tie gating buys at most one round per Max vertex
(\Cref{thm:zero-timer,cor:gate}; whether every degenerate one-player run
obeys $L\le h^{\ast}_{\mathrm{HK}}$ is open); the first blow-up level and a layer
translated by a single coordinate are realised by one-player games and the
second level by a two-player game on $138$ vertices
(\Cref{rem:blowup-measured,prop:b2-realised}), so the third level is the
smallest open instance --- its outer half is realised on $194$ vertices,
its translated layer remains unrealised (\Cref{prop:b3-outer}) and out
of reach of the pinned shape, which cannot double
(\Cref{cor:pinned-no-doubling}) --- and
\Cref{rem:blowup-realise} states the question
that remains: whether each further level costs only a constant number of
controlled vertices and of bits; the height of a blow-up is exactly
$2h+2$ (\Cref{prop:blowup-height}); and its walk needs the Min vertex
(\Cref{thm:b2-walk}). The one-player ceiling reads $1,2,4,6,\ge10,\ge12,\ge13$
and the Holt--Klee ceiling $1,2,4,6,11,\ge14,\ge20$
(\Cref{rem:four-ceilings}), both growing by at least one per dimension
(\Cref{prop:sink-lift,prop:oneplayer-plus-one}); stacking copies of a
block behind a freezing bias gives one-player games with $7k$ Max
vertices and runs of length $12k$, so the one-player ceiling exceeds
$|\Vmax|$ by a constant factor, though the run of a stack never exceeds,
per state, that of its best block (\Cref{thm:stack,cor:stack-family}); the blow-up
doubles and stays Holt--Klee at $m=4,5$ when its outer pair reads a
non-parity signal, and a readout-free condition stops the iteration on
every tall seed examined (\Cref{prop:blowup-readout,prop:hk-doubling-measured,prop:tstar});
at $m=3$ every class is realised, the two non-Holt--Klee ones with one
Min vertex (\Cref{prop:m3-realised,prop:m3-one-min}); readouts make the search practice a theorem,
one player buying affine readouts and the second concavity
(\Cref{thm:readout-realise,thm:min-count}); and the profile cube is a
unique sink orientation with the Max cube as its sink projection
(\Cref{thm:profile-uso,prop:projection}), Holt--Klee constraining that
projection not at all wherever checked ($m\le3$, the height-$7$ orbit,
$B^{2}$); Lemke's algorithm on the profile complementarity problem is a
bias homotopy, on one player an improving multi-switch rule that no
barrier here covers and on two players a directed path of the profile
cube; no hard family of this document costs it more than about $2|C|$
switches, while four explicit one-player witnesses exceed $|C|$, with
$7$ breakpoints at $|C|=3$ and $22$ at $|C|=6$
(\Cref{sec:bias,prop:bias-witnesses}); Condon's damping read as a homotopy
in the survival probability is a different path, exponentially long on
$\mathrm{BP}(D)$ and on \Cref{thm:fold}'s $P_D$ with a two-vertex seed
(\Cref{prop:discount-fold,cor:two-homotopies}). Bidirectional improvement and its
one-sided form, best-response restart, are covered by no barrier here and
have no known superpolynomial family
(\Cref{def:bsi,thm:bsi-nostall,prop:bsi-br}); the restart rule beats the
all-switches ceiling already at two Max vertices (\Cref{prop:w2}) and is
polynomial whenever every Min vertex has a constant option
(\Cref{cor:clamp}), though both rules take
$\Theta(N)$ rounds where all-switches takes one, and both tracks of a
long run must be long (\Cref{thm:readout,thm:bsi-tracks}); the
window barrier covers rules solving $O(N^{\alpha})$ average vertices for
$\alpha<1$ and no more (\Cref{thm:window-barrier}); the degree-two
Lasserre lift and the interior-point route are closed
(\Cref{thm:lasserre-vacuous}, \Cref{rem:lcp}), the choice-variable
Sherali--Adams lift fails one level later (\Cref{rem:choice-lift}), and
handicap zero neither depends on the base profile nor bounds the run,
attaining the one-player ceiling at $m\le4$
(\Cref{rem:handicap-base,prop:handicap-zero-ceiling}); and the wedge of
\Cref{sec:wedge} is the state of the art on the negative side, defeating
the slack, transitive, transport and hybrid mechanisms for a proved
$2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds while the seeded calculus and the simulation
preorder decide it at once (\Cref{thm:wedge-proved,rem:wedge}); beyond it,
$\mathrm{BC}(e,s)$ of \Cref{sec:bc} keeps every propagation mechanism, in
both registers and in any combination, silent for a proved
$2^{\Omega(N)}$ operator applications at its one value-distinguishing
vertex, the simulation preorder silent outright, and their combination
silent as long by measurement, while one policy evaluation decides it
(\Cref{thm:bc-lower}), so what is exponentially separated there is the
propagation side from one linear solve.

\paragraph{Mechanisms, and where they stop.} Six sound polynomial-time
comparison mechanisms are developed and measured against one yardstick:
three that \emph{propagate} --- the simulation preorder, the slack
calculus and the ratio calculus, the second being an endpoint of a
continuum containing the third --- the seeded calculus, the transport
programme and the hybrid.
The value-simulation preorder (\Cref{sec:simorder}) is arithmetic-free,
returns all $N^{2}$ verdicts from one greatest fixed point, and beats
every bounded radius; it fails on branch compensation, and
\Cref{thm:matching-barrier} shows every calculus of its class fails, by a
fooling pair. The slack calculus (\Cref{sec:slack}) repairs exactly that
and is cheap in precision, but on non-interacting parts it is two-sided
value iteration (\Cref{thm:slack-barrier}), hence exponential on an
explicit family for the pair test, the own-successor test's stall there
being measured only (\Cref{cor:slack-stalls}) --- for its own clauses
only: adding transitivity, whose
premises leave the separated region, turns that family from
$2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds into $4m-3$ for $m\le9$ (\Cref{prop:trans-Hm}), and no
stall at a value-distinguishing vertex of a stopping game is permanent,
since the calculus computes $w^\ast$ exactly in the limit
(\Cref{thm:trans-complete}; at vertices whose successors have equal value
the calculi may stay silent for ever, harmlessly, \Cref{prop:fv-stall}). Permanent
blindness exists only where $T$ has a second fixed point, hence never on
a stopping game (\Cref{lem:phi-certificate,prop:trans-stall}), and that
is not an accident: a certificate of the form $\varphi(x)-\psi(y)$ that
survives a min-plus closure must have $\varphi=\psi$ and must be a
fixed point of $T$, so on a stopping game it is the exact limit and
proves nothing about the rate (\Cref{thm:separable}); what escapes has
vanishing diagonal (\Cref{cor:set-certificate}). Read multiplicatively
instead of additively, the same calculus becomes the ratio calculus of
\Cref{sec:ratio}, which is incomparable with the additive one pair by
pair, is not an instance of it --- its average clauses are the harmonic
mean and the mediant --- and yet obeys the same two-sided bound on the
same configurations and stalls on the same family for the same number of
rounds (\Cref{thm:ratio-sandwich,cor:ratio-stall}); the two are the ends
of a one-parameter family every member of which stalls identically,
because the quantity deciding whether the bound is informative does not
involve the parameter (\Cref{prop:mobius}). The classical nonlinear
Perron--Frobenius route that motivates the multiplicative reading is
closed separately: after homogenisation the cone spectral radius is
identically $1$, and the Collatz--Wielandt bracket can be $[1,2]$ while
the vector it brackets is off by $2^{N-3}$ (\Cref{prop:cw}). Seeding it from an evaluated bracket
(\Cref{sec:seeded}) strictly dominates the additive calculus, and its
one-round variant decides every stalling instance in this document, the
wedge included (\Cref{prop:seeded-decides}, \Cref{rem:wedge}) --- while
\Cref{thm:seeded-barrier} shows the geometry, not the initialisation, was
always the obstruction. The transport mechanism does not propagate at all:
the linear program of \Cref{sec:transport} optimises over the monotonicity
$Tw^\ast=w^\ast$ imposes, is exact on successor-closed average parts
(\Cref{lem:transport-exact}), and therefore settles in one solve both
$G_8$ and the family $H_m$ on which the slack calculus stalls
exponentially. On a seven-vertex game it fails in turn to order the two
successors, and \Cref{thm:transport-barrier} shows why that failure is
complementary: what defeats the pair test is a controlled vertex lying on a
cycle through the branch it must be compared against, and there the test needs
its seed to localise the value to within $2^{-\Theta(N)}$. It is a pair test
that fails, not the mechanism: \Cref{rem:own-successor} observes that at a Max
vertex the row $x(v)\ge x(v^{(i)})$ is already in $Q(G)$, so a negative
$\operatorname{Sep}(v,v^{(i)})$ decides $v$ outright, and it is negative on
every instance in question; the two readings are incomparable, the
own-successor separators vanishing on $R$ of \Cref{prop:own-stall} where the
pair test decides, and a seven-vertex game keeps one vertex silent under
both until two others are decided (\Cref{rem:own-stall}).

Since the two failures are complementary, the two mechanisms are coupled
in \Cref{sec:hybrid}, and the coupling is a feedback loop rather than a
disjunction: the halves of the controlled rows that a linear programme
must drop, being non-convex, are exactly the ones whose consequences for
differences the slack clauses supply, while the average equalities the
slack calculus can only reach by iterating are what the programme solves
in one step. The result is the sharpest mechanism here.
\Cref{thm:hybrid-complete} shows that on a stopping game every closed
matrix has $K(\Delta)=\{w^\ast\}$ --- the polytope collapses to the value
vector, not merely to a decided pair --- and that the argument touches the
programme only at the $4N$ pairs containing a sink
(\Cref{cor:hybrid-sink}), so the hybrid is a box-propagation loop whose
only fixed point is the exact one. It disposes of every stall recorded before it, in one round or two
(\Cref{prop:hybrid-decides}); the wedge of \Cref{sec:wedge}, recorded
after it, defeats it for $2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds (\Cref{thm:wedge-proved}). What is
left is a rate, and \Cref{prop:hybrid-rate} reduces even that to the decay
of one scalar sequence: the box has width $\omega_k$, a bound
$\omega_k\le C\rho^{k}$ with $\rho\le1-1/\mathrm{poly}(N)$ would put the
problem in $\mathsf P$, and $\rho\to1$ is the only way the mechanism can
fail, and we do not know whether it does. What we do know is that the
evidence suggesting it does not was worthless: every early instance on which the hybrid was measured had one controlled
vertex, a class \Cref{prop:hybrid-onectrl} shows is decided at round two
for free; the ten-vertex stall $R$ with five controlled vertices
(\Cref{prop:hybrid-decides}) and the two-Max-vertex families of
\Cref{thm:hybrid-lower} and \Cref{sec:wedge} are the exceptions, and the
last defeats it. \Cref{thm:hybrid-convex-barrier} supplies the method for lower
bounds --- a shrinking chain of convex certificates, replacing the box
certificate of \Cref{thm:slack-barrier}, which \Cref{rem:hybrid-barrier-lift}
shows is provably too weak here --- and \Cref{thm:hybrid-lower} applies it
to a one-player family with two Max vertices wired crosswise, each keeping
the other's box open, on which $\Delta_k(v^{(0)},v^{(1)})$ stays
nonnegative for $2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds. That is not a refutation:
\Cref{rem:own-successor} observes that a decision rule should compare a
controlled vertex with its \emph{own} successor rather than its two
successors with each other, and on that family the useful entry is
negative at round one. The same correction applies to
\Cref{prop:transport-stalls} and \Cref{thm:transport-barrier}, which order
successors and do not stall as decision rules.

\paragraph{The gap.} \Cref{def:missing} states it at full strength and
\Cref{thm:gap-equivalence} proves it equivalent to a polynomial-time
algorithm. The same is true of a formally weaker-looking demand: a rule
that resolves a single vertex per round, on a game it may simplify in
between, already suffices (\Cref{thm:decide-one-bit}). What is
\emph{not} established anywhere above is a polynomial-time mechanism for
producing even one of the $n$ comparison bits of
\Cref{thm:compare-equivalence} --- equivalently, for selecting a
switchable vertex at which the current strategy is wrong. And that bit is
irreducibly global: two instances agreeing on the radius-$k$
neighbourhood of a vertex, for any fixed $k$, can reverse the answer
there (\Cref{prop:locality}). That is the whole of the remaining gap, and
it is the whole of the problem. \Cref{thm:seed-dichotomy} locates it once
more: any instance defeating the seeded rule with polynomially many
improvement rounds is a stopping SSG on which switch-all improvement
itself runs superpolynomially long, which no construction here achieves.
And every exponential lower bound above is a bound on the rounds of a
rule, never on information: an optimal strategy is found from $|C|+2$
adaptively chosen evaluations, at the price of solving the hypothesis
between them (\Cref{thm:eval-queries}); the order of the average values
is a unique certificate (\Cref{rem:order-unique}), and no certificate
format closes the gap (\Cref{prop:bracket}).

\end{document}
